To those members of the Massachusetts Bench and Bar who have been particularly helpful in the compilation of this work, in grateful appreciation of their encouragement and friendly guidance throughout the years.

PREFACE 

During the course of many years' experience in the Criminal Court, it became clear to me that members of Bench and Bar, but particularly police officers, might benefit from a digest of Massachusetts criminal cases. Such a digest would constitute a collection of all decisions arising out of the criminal law which have practical value today in the administration of justice throughout the Commonwealth. Such a digest would condense the decisions, place each under the heading most applicable to the case and enable the searcher to find readily the law in point. 

By preserving the essential significance of each decision, it would enable the law enforcement officer to find at a glance the answers to his questions. It would enable all those who must conduct the time-consuming search for the right case to find it with speed and certainty. Such a digest would also serve as a convenient guide to the official Reports in all matters involving the criminal law.

With such a digest in mind, the following cases have been collected, condensed and classified. If in any way they serve to clarify the practice, procedure and substantive law of crimes in Massachusetts, or if they prove to be especially useful to police officers and law enforcement agencies throughout the State, then their main purpose will have been accomplished. 

HARVEY B. HUDSON

FOREWORD 

Ever since my appointment to serve as Associate Justice of the Municipal Criminal Court of the City of Boston, I have had the privilege of knowing Harvey Burt Hudson who, as a Clerk, has been connected with that Court for forty-six years. 

It is hardly necessary for me to stress here the efficiency with which he has served both the Court and the public during that time. Many have been the occasions when he has ably assisted both myself and my associates, calling upon his rich fund of knowledge on every conceivable angle of the criminal law. His help to police officers has been no less tangible, although that also is too well known to require endorsement here.

Thus it is obvious that the present volume is the culminating result of Mr. Hudson's many years of extensive experience with Massachusetts criminal cases. As an aid to police officers, as a carefully and logically indexed guide to the official Reports, and as the only local digest of all the criminal cases which are still law in this State, the following compilation should fulfill a long-felt want among the law books of the Commonwealth.

MICHAEL J. MURRAY 

Boston 

July 20, 1939

ABDUCTION GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 272, SECS. 2 and 3 

Com. v. Cook, 12 Met. 93 

Fraudulently enticing a girl from her father's house for the purposes of prostitution does not apply to a case where she was enticed for the sole purpose of illicit intercourse with the defendant alone. 

Com. v. Taschetta, 252 Mass. 158

If the complaint alleges that the woman enticed is of chaste life, that allegation must be proved by the commonwealth. 

ABORTION GEN. LAWS, (TER. ED.), CHAP. 272, SEC. 19 

Form of complaint. 

Com. v. Corkin, 136 Mass. 429 

Com. v. Wood, 11 Gray, 85

A complaint alleging that A. B. on the first day of April in the year of our Lord one thousand nine hundred and thirty-seven at the City of Boston aforesaid and within the judicial district of said court did unlawfully use a certain instrument, a more particular description of the same being to the said complainant unknown, which said instrument the said (deft.) in his hands had and held, by then and there forcing and thrusting the said instrument into the body and womb of a certain woman whose name is Mary Jones, with the intent thereby then and there to cause and procure the miscarriage of said Jones against the peace etc. sufficiently describes the offense. 

Pleading as to pregnancy. 

Com. v. Taylor, 132 Mass. 261 

Com. v. Follansbee, 155 Mass. 274 

Com. v. Tibbetts, 157 Mass. 519

In a complaint for abortion, an attempt to procure the miscarriage of a woman, it is not necessary to the maintenance of the complaint that she should be pregnant. 1

CRIMINAL CASES Com. v. Surles, 165 Mass. 59 

If an averment of pregnancy is alleged, that fact must be proved. 

Com. v. Jacobs, 9 Allen, 275

The using of an instrument to procure an abortion extends to the case in which the woman is not pregnant. 

Pleading as to death ensuing. 

Com. v. Adams, 127 Mass. 15 

An allegation in a complaint for abortion that death ensued is only an aggravation of the offense charged; without this allegation a complete offense is set out in the complaint. 

Com. v. Homer, 153 Mass. 343

The pleader need not allege that death ensued. 

Com. v. Thompson, 108 Mass. 461

The pleader need not allege that the woman operated on did or did not die. 

Com. v. Jackson, 15 Gray, 187 

Where the woman died, the complaint need not set forth the offense as a murder in technical form. 

Date of the complaint. 

Com. v. Snow, 116 Mass. 47

The date of the commission of the crime is not material. 

Legal abortion. 

Com. v. Nason, 252 Mass. 545

A physician has the right to perform an abortion if it is done on the best judgment of the physician, and his judgment corresponds with the average judgment of physicians in the community.

If the defendant who is a physician gave a woman who is a codefendant advice and she acted upon it, she is not guilty; but if he was giving advice that she did not know of and was taking on her own enterprise and undertaking to do something whereby she was not covered by a physician's advice she is guilty.

CRIMINAL CASES 

3 Accomplice. 

Com. v. Turner, 224 Mass, 229 

Com. v. Boynton, 116 Mass. 343

A person whose death was caused by an abortion is not an accomplice and a defendant may be convicted upon the dying declaration, even though unsupported.

Com. v. Follansbee, 155 Mass. 274 

A woman operated upon is not an accomplice. 

Com. v. Drake, 124 Mass. 21

One who goes with another woman to defendant's house and the other woman has an operation performed and who goes two days afterwards to defendant's house and remains there for two days, is not an accomplice. 

Naming the instrument. 

Com. v. Donoghue, 266 Mass. 391

Not naming the instrument and not pleading name unknown, is cured by a bill of particulars. 

Accessory before and after. 

Com. v. Donoghue, 266 Mass. 391

A man who was responsible for the condition of a woman as to pregnancy, gave the woman some money to hire a doctor to perform the operation, the hiring of the doctor by the woman was a hiring by the defendant and he could be convicted of being an accessory before the fact. 

Com. v. Leger, 264 Mass. 217

One arranging for the attendance upon a woman with knowledge of her pregnancy and purposes, is guilty of being an accessory; the woman could testify that she thought she was pregnant and that the defendant told her to lie down and he inserted something inside of her. 

Dying declarations.

GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 233, SEC. 64 

Com. v. Wagner, 231 Mass. 265

Whether dying declarations are oral or written, they are admissible.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Smith, 213 Mass. 563

Statements made to the attending physician in order that he might give advice are admissible. In dying declarations the word "abortion" is admissible. 

Com. v. Thompson, 159 Mass. 56

A mother may testify as to dying declarations. 

Com. v. Cantor, 253 Mass. 509

It is a question for the jury as to whether the woman knew she was going to die.

Evidence. 

Com. v. Fenno, 134 Mass. 217

A statement made by a woman to a man, "I feel weak," after coming from a house where she had been for a period of thirty minutes, is admissible against the defendant as tending to show an operation was performed, and admissions of woman indicating pain were admissible for that purpose. 

Com. v. Fitch, 132 Mass. 22

The deceased had informed a person a short time previous to the alleged offense that the defendant was not the father and that if she could not get it done she would do it herself —— not admissible as hearsay. 

Com. v. Brown, 121 Mass. 69 

The commonwealth can introduce instruments found in the house of the defendant. Medical experts can testify that they were adapted for producing abortion, although they could be used for other purposes. Medical books cannot be read in evidence to the jury. 

Com. v. Nason, 252 Mass. 545

It is immaterial that the foetus had lost vitality and could never have developed into a living child. 

Com. v. Barrows, 176 Mass. 17

Cards found in the possession of the defendant are admissible in evidence. 

Com. v. Corkin, 136 Mass. 429

The defendant administered treatment on two different occasions prior to the date of the complaint. It is competent to

CRIMINAL CASES 

5 show intent and his knowledge of the pregnant condition of the woman. 

Com. v. Lombardo, 271 Mass. 41

The mere buying of pills by the defendant for a woman would not be sufficient to warrant a conviction, even if they were to be used by a girl for the purposes set forth in the complaint, unless the defendant secured them for her and administered and prescribed them with the intent on his part to procure a miscarriage. 

Com. v. Morrison, 16 Gray, 224

With intent to procure an abortion, causing a woman to take medicine: It is not necessary to aver or prove the name of the medicine, or that it was noxious. The only overt act to be proved is the advice to her to take the medicine. 

Com. v. Sholes, 13 Allen, 554

The pleader need not aver that the act was done maliciously and without any lawful justification. It is sufficient to allege that it was unlawfully done with intent to procure and cause the miscarriage. 

Com. v. Blair, 126 Mass. 40

On a trial for abortion there was evidence that wounds, cuts, and bruises were found on the womb of the woman on whom the operation was performed, and the defendant had the opportunity to commit the crime. Evidence is admissible that five months before the alleged crime the defendant had in his possession instruments which he described as fitted to procure an abortion. 

Advertising for abortion and instruments.

CHAP. 272, SEC. 20 

Com. v. Hartford, 193 Mass. 464 

Com. v. Allison, 227 Mass. 57 

Form for. Guilty knowledge is not essential. 

Insurance policy on woman. 

Hatch v. Ins. Co., 120 Mass. 550

No recovery can be had on an insurance policy if death results from having voluntarily assented to an illegal operation, known by her to be dangerous to life, without any justifiable medical reason.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Co-defendants. 

Com. v. Sinclair, 195 Mass. 100

A defendant who is present may be convicted the same as the principal.

ABUSE OF A FEMALE CHILD GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 265, SEC. 23 

See Evidence and Rape. 

Com. v. Hollis, 170 Mass. 433

To constitute carnal knowledge there must be penetration, but not necessarily emission. In this case the hymen was intact. 

Com. v. Hackett, 170 Mass. 194

Abuse of a female child under the age of 16 years is rape, but it is pleaded as an abuse of a female child. 

Com. v. Hollis, 170 Mass. 433 

The jury may judge the age of the girl if her age is not testified to. 

Com. v. Cotting, 248 Mass. 401

It does not matter at the trial whether she had had connection with other men previously. 

Com. v. Murphy, 165 Mass. 66

It does not make any difference whether the defendant knew or did not know the age of the girl. 

Com. v. Duff, 245 Mass. 81

The commonwealth offered evidence that the child was pregnant in order to show she had connection. The defendant should have been allowed then to offer testimony that he was not the man who was responsible for her pregnancy. 

Com. v. Rosnell, 143 Mass. 32

It is no defense that the child consented to the acts of the defendant. Need not aver or prove that the acts were against her will, or without her consent. She is incapable of giving her consent, and it will be conclusively presumed that she did not consent.

CRIMINAL CASES 

7 

ABUSE OF PROCESS 

McLean v. Naumkeag Trust Co., 268 Mass. 437

To make out a case of the abuse of legal process there must be some particular act or omission which amounts to a misuse or oppression beyond the mere fact of arrest and detention. 

ACCESSORY BEFORE AND AFTER THE FACT 

Before.

GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 274, SECS. 2-5 INCL. 

Com. v. Asherbrouski, 196 Mass. 342

One indicted as accessory before the fact to an unknown person who is alleged to have committed the crime of burning property with intent to defraud the insurer cannot be convicted unless it is proven that the burning was done with the intent alleged. Nor can he be convicted even though he procured the burning to be done if the person who did it at the time was ignorant of the existence of the insurance on the property. 

Com. v. Desatnick, 262 Mass. 408

Before the fact to a first-degree murder. The principal never was arrested. Accessory was convicted. May be tried, indicted, and punished in the county where the principal felon may be indicted and tried, although the procuring of the felony took place elsewhere. 

Com. v. Pettes, 114 Mass. 307

One who procures a felony to be committed in a county of this commonwealth by means of letters written elsewhere can be convicted in that county as an accessory before the fact. 

Com. v. Knapp, 9 Pick. 496

To be present aiding and abetting, the abettor must be in a position where he may actually aid. It is not enough that he was at a place appointed where the principal erroneously supposed he might render aid. 

Com. v. Spezzaro, 250 Mass. 454

There was evidence of a felony having been committed. The defendant was seen standing near a car which had stopped in

CRIMINAL CASES 

front of a dwelling house containing stolen property. The defendant had ownership in the car. He knew the principal and was frequently seen in his company. Enough to convict. 

Com. v. Sokarellis, 254 Mass. 454

In order to convict of being an accessory after the fact the commonwealth was bound to prove at the trial beyond a reasonable doubt that the defendant was not within the exceptions described in the indictment. 

Com. v. Felton, 101 Mass. 204

If the crime for which the principal is charged is not a crime in this state, an accessory cannot be convicted. 

Com. v. Wallace, 108 Mass. 12

One who is charged with having aided and abetted in concealing mortgaged property, the averment of aiding and abetting can be rejected as surplusage. The accessory can be charged as a principal. 

Com. v. Glover, 111 Mass. 395

On an indictment for being accessory to a crime committed by an unknown person the judge instructed the jury that if the name of the principal was known to the grand jury it was a variance; that the fact that such name was known to them need not be proved affirmatively by the commonwealth, but if there was evidence in the matter, the burden of proving that it was known was on the commonwealth. 

ACKNOWLEDGMENT OF SATISFACTION GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 276, SEC. 55 

Bryant v. Rich's Grill, 216 Mass. 344

The defendant was arrested for discrimination on account of color; entered into an agreement through his attorney with the attorney for the complainant that if he (the defendant) would plead guilty and receive a fine he would waive civil damages; good agreement and not unlawful. 

Stevens v. Hawthorne, 12 Allen, 402

An acknowledgment of satisfaction in a criminal case is not a bar to a civil action for the same crime.

CRIMINAL CASES 

9 

Dowdicans Bail Case, 115 Mass. 133

One indicted for assault and battery and files an acknowledgment, this does not entitle the defendant to a discharge. 

Partridge v. Hood, 120 Mass. 403

An action will not lie upon an agreement entered into for the purpose of compounding a misdemeanor, unless it appears that satisfaction has been acknowledged in and approved by the court in which the prosecution was pending. 

ACCOMPLICE Com. v. Follansbee, 155 Mass. 274 

A woman operated upon for an abortion is not an accomplice. A woman who procured ether without any knowledge that it was to be used unlawfully is not an accomplice. 

Com. v. Baker, 155 Mass. 287

One who goes into a gambling house and plays for the purpose of using the evidence obtained therein is not an accomplice. 

Com. v. Downing, 4 Gray, 29

One who purchases liquor for the express purpose of prosecuting the seller is not an accomplice, but the jury should be instructed to receive his testimony with the greatest caution and mistrust. 

Com. v. Leger, 264 Mass. 217 

Com. v. Bosworth, 22 Pick. 397

The general rule of practice is to caution the jury not to convict where the evidence consists of the uncorroborated testimony of an accomplice. 

Com. v. Devaney, 182 Mass. 33 

Com. v. Kelley, 186 Mass. 403

The evidence of an accomplice who is indicted but not apprehended is admissible if they were indicted jointly. 

Com. v. Bishop, 165 Mass. 148

The principal witness for the commonwealth is an accomplice. There is no rule requiring the court to instruct the jury as to the weight to be given to his testimony. In an accomplice no corroboration is required as a matter of law.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Ford, 111 Mass. 394

It is a question of fact for the jury to regard a witness as an accomplice. It is not necessary that he should be so proved beyond a reasonable doubt. 

Com. v. Holmes, 127 Mass. 424

Evidence to corroborate the testimony of an accomplice must tend to connect the accused with the crime. It is competent for the jury to convict on the testimony of an accomplice alone, but the source of this evidence is so corrupt that it is always looked upon with suspicion and jealousy, hence the court even considers it its duty to advise the jury to acquit. 

Com. v. Lavery, 255 Mass. 327

The defendant was waiting in an automobile while another man did the breaking and entering, then the defendant drove the man away. Guilty not as an accomplice but as a principal. 

ADMISSIONS 

Com. v. Mantir, 208 Mass. 71 

Com. v. Spiropoulos, 208 Mass. 74 

Com. v. Kenney, 12 Met. 235

If a defendant while under arrest is charged with a crime by an accusation made in his presence and hearing and he remains mute or unequivocally denies it, his silence or denial is not admissible in evidence against him. But if he makes an equivocal reply, the question or statement by which it was elicited and the answer or comment are admissible. 

Com. v. Robinson, 165 Mass. 426 

The failure to caution that anything said by the defendant may be used against him does not render the admission incompetent. 

Com. v. Osman, 284 Mass. 421

Two defendants were arrested and accused each other in the presence of a police officer. Denials were made. This was admissible. 

Com. v. Mitchell, 117 Mass. 431

An officer when arresting the defendant for assault and battery upon a girl asked the defendant if the girl had been to his house. The reply was no. The officer then said the more lies one

CRIMINAL CASES 

11 tells the oftener one gets into mud. The defendant then admitted she had been to his house and he had given her some money. Admissible. It was not induced through fear nor were there any threats made. 

Com. v. White, 208 Mass. 202

In case it is to be used against himself, in a complaint for bribery admission can be used against all the defendants. 

Com. v. Ingraham, 7 Gray, 46

Two persons were indicted for setting a fire. Evidence of the admission of one of the defendants in conversation with another person three months after the fire that he was with the other defendant when he set the fire. The admission is good as against him but not as to the other defendant. 

Com. v. Porter, 237 Mass. 1

Evidence of silence on the part of the defendant when he was informed of indecent and unnatural acts by men in his house was held to have been admissible as tending to show an admission of guilt. 

Com. v. O'Brien, 179 Mass. 533

The defendant's failure to reply to a statement made in his presence concerning a material matter and under such circumstances as to call for a reply, or a denial, is admissible against the defendant. 

Com. v. Riches, 219 Mass. 433

Admissions and declarations by one defendant can be used against the other. The insertion of an advertisement in a paper by one defendant is by both, provided both shared in the money obtained. 

Com. v. Anderson, 220 Mass. 142

The defendant accused of a crime answered by false statements. It is a question for the jury to determine, said accusations being true and the answers false; also whether such false statements were made because the defendant was conscious of her own guilt or for some other reason. 

Com. v. Reibstein, 257 Mass. 436

Any statement freely made by the defendant when under arrest which tends to show his guilt is admissible. A defendant under arrest has the right to remain silent or to deny accusations and in that case the evidence cannot be used.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Graham, 279 Mass. 466

The defendant was brought to the bedside of the assaulted party; the assaulted party was asked, "Did the defendant cut you?" and she replied, "He must have, there was no one else there." The defendant was then asked, "What do you say about that?” “I must have been crazy if I did it." Admissible, showing an equivocal reply. 

Com. v. Gleason, 262 Mass. 185

An admission is a statement by the accused tending to prove his guilt. A confession is an acknowledgment of guilt. 

Com. v. Trefethen, 157 Mass. 180

A statement in the presence of the defendant to which no reply is made is not admissible, unless it appears that he was at liberty to make a reply, and that the statement was made by such a person and under such circumstances as to materially call for one, unless he intends to admit it. But if he makes a reply wholly or partially admitting the truth of the facts stated, both the statement and the reply are competent. 

Com. v. Hayward, 247 Mass. 16 

Com. v. Merrick, 255 Mass. 510

The distinction between an admission and a confession as applied in criminal law is not a technical refinement but is based upon the substantive difference of the character of the evidence educed from each. A confession is a direct acknowledgment of guilt on the part of the accused and by the very force of the definition excludes an admission, which of itself as applied in criminal law is a statement by the accused, direct or implied, of facts pertinent to the issue, and tending, in connection with proof of other facts, to prove his guilt, but of itself is insufficient to authorize a conviction. 

Com. v. McCabe, 163 Mass. 98

If a statement is made in the hearing and presence of a person which affected his rights or was incriminating to him, and he did not reply to it or make any explanation but remained silent, such silence was tantamount to an admission of the truth of the facts contained in the statement made, provided the same was heard and understood by such person and he was not in custody or under restraint but at liberty to reply or explain, and provided such statement was of such nature and made under such circumstances and by such persons as naturally to call for a reply.

CRIMINAL CASES 

13 

Com. v. Jokinen, 257 Mass. 429

The defendant was arrested for robbery. He thought he was arrested for gambling and no formal complaint had been made. 

He was not informed of his rights. This is not sufficient to render statements involuntarily made inadmissible. The defendant can refuse to speak while under arrest but his declarations were not involuntary because made after his arrest. A confession of guilt made by the defendant to the arresting officers is prima facie voluntarily. 

Com. v. Morris, 264 Mass. 314 

Com. v. Hamel, 264 Mass. 564

The defendant (not under arrest) was requested to go to a hospital for identification. There the woman upon whom he had performed an operation for abortion made certain statements in his presence to an officer. In replying to a question "he said that he knew the woman but he had nothing to say." It was a question for the jury whether this was admissible as an admission. 

Com. v. McDermott, 123 Mass. 440

A and B were arrested for larceny of a watch from the person. 

In a search at the station house the watch was found on A. On being asked how he came by it he replied B gave it to him. B had nothing to say. B had the right while in custody to keep silent and was not called upon to contradict or reply to any statement made in his hearing. No inference against him was warranted by his failure to deny the truth of what A said to the officer.

ADULTERATED FOOD GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 94 

See under Evidence and Pleading. 

Com. v. N. E. Syrup Co., 217 Mass. 432

The statute declaring food adulterated if it contains any antiseptic or preservative substance, excepting certain substances, the mixing of cane sugar as a preservative is prohibited. The selling of "Golden Tree Syrup," the same being a blend, is not a crime under Chapter 94 of selling sirup in imitation or under the name of maple sirup.

CRIMINAL CASES Farrell v. Manhattan Market, 198 Mass. 271 

Provisions purchased from a dealer, leaving the selection to the dealer, and the goods are unfit for food, the dealer is liable in a civil action. 

Adams v. N. D. Syrup Co., 210 Mass. 475

Maple sugar, consisting of part maple and part granulated sugar, sold as blended maple is not adulteration or imitation under the statute. 

Com. v. Privite, 292 Mass. 378

The complaint was for selling a misbranded product. The count was based on Section 187, which was intended to protect the particular purchaser from deception, and was not directed at a package or label which "bears any statement, design or device regarding the ingredients or the substances contained therein which is false or misleading in any particular." That is dealt with in the following section, Sec. 188. The facts do not sustain a conviction. The inspector who purchased the product was not misled or deceived. Pleading.

ADULTERY GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 272, SEC. 14 

Com. v. Fuller, 163 Mass. 499

The offense must be charged as a single offense. Not continuando. 

Com. v. Tompson, 2 Cush. 551

If the averment charges intercourse with a woman, she not being the lawful wife of the defendant, it is a sufficient averment. 

Com. v. Reardon, 6 Cush. 78

If the indictment against the man avers adultery with a married woman, not the lawful wife of the defendant, it is not necessary to aver that the defendant was married or unmarried. If the indictment alleges the woman was the wife of another man, this averment is sufficient. 

Moore v. Com., 6 Met. 243

The pleader must aver that the man and the woman were not married to each other. Any form which charges she was the wife of somebody else, other than the accused, is sufficient.

CRIMINAL CASES 

15 

Date of offense. 

Com. v. Cobb, 14 Gray, 57 

Com. v. Merriam, 14 Pick. 518

A day must be alleged in the indictment, but the commonwealth need not prove that the crime was committed on the very day alleged. If the proof shows that the act was committed on any day within the period fixed by the statute of limitations it is sufficient. 

Com. v. Lahey, 14 Gray, 91

Evidence of another distinct act of adultery cannot be introduced, where but one act upon a single day charged is the subject of the trial, but evidence is admissible to show familiarity between the parties on a previous occasion. 

Other acts of adultery. 

Com. v. Nicholas, 114 Mass. 285 

Com. v. Russeau, 140 Mass. 363

Evidence of other acts of adultery committed by the same parties near the time charged, although in another state, is admissible to support the indictment. 

Thayer v. Thayer, 101 Mass. 111

Acts committed outside of the state and subsequent to the filing of the libel as tending to prove the possibility of intercourse and of their adulterous tendencies are admissible. 2 Gray, 354, and 11 Gray, 450 discussed. 

Com. v. Curtis, 97 Mass. 574

Evidence is admissible as to relations both prior and subsequent to the date alleged, whether she gave birth four months after and whether they had lived together nine months previous. 

Com. v. Pierce, 11 Gray, 447

Evidence of acts of familiarity between the parties before the date of the complaint and subsequently is admissible. 

Com. v. Merriam, 14 Pick. 518

A witness who witnessed the acts was impeached by the defendant, and the court admitted testimony of other acts of familiarity to corroborate his testimony, one a short time previous and one a year previous.

CRIMINAL CASES 

44444 1 Suspicion. 

White v. Ely, 234 Mass. 221

Though persons seen in each other's company frequently and occupying the same house, that is not enough evidence to warrant a conviction. Mere suspicion is not proof. 

Presumption of death. 

Com. v. Thompson, 11 Allen, 23 

Whippen v. Whippen, 147 Mass. 294

A man may be convicted of adultery who in good faith in the belief that she is a widow, marries and cohabits with a woman who has left her husband and remained absent more than seven years without hearing from him, if in fact her husband is still living. 

Com. v. Thompson, 6 Allen, 591

A man in good faith marries a woman whose husband has remained away more than seven years without being heard from and is believed to be dead, and if believed to be dead he cannot be convicted of adultery if in fact her husband is living. 

If in fact she left her husband this rule does not apply. 

After a divorce. 

Com. v. Kendall, 162 Mass. 221

The defendant left the city and obtained a divorce in South 

Dakota; he can be convicted of adultery here. 

Moors v. Moors, 121 Mass. 232

One who marries before the divorce becomes absolute, in good faith, is guilty of adultery if he marries again. 

Chastity of the woman. 

Com. v. Gray, 129 Mass. 474

Evidence of the chastity of the woman with whom the defendant is alleged to have committed the crime is competent. 

Evidence. 

Com. v. Morrisey, 175 Mass. 264

A confession that the defendant had intercourse with a woman in a bastardy case need not be corroborated.

CRIMINAL CASES 

17 

Com. v. Hussey, 157 Mass. 415

The defendant is guilty if the body of the male penetrated the body of the female. Emission is not essential. 

Com. v. Clifford, 145 Mass. 97

The charge: a married man is found with a woman not his wife, in a room with a bed in it and stays throughout the night with her. That is sufficient to warrant a finding of guilty against him. 

Com. v. Bakeman, 131 Mass. 577

A man may be convicted although at the time the act was committed the woman was in such a state of stupefaction as to be incapable of consent. 

Com. v. Bowers, 121 Mass. 45

The defendants were discovered in a bedroom of a hotel at midnight under circumstances which if unexplained would warrant a jury in finding them guilty of adultery. The defendants could not introduce evidence that they in conversation with some other person told them that they were going there for an innocent purpose. 

Com. v. Thompson, 99 Mass. 444

A man and a woman were charged with adultery. She admitted that she was a married woman, in the absence of the man. This is not competent evidence against the man. 

Com. v. Elwell, 2 Met. 190

The commonwealth need not prove intent on the part of an unmarried man with a married woman. 

Com. v. Squires, 97 Mass. 59

An indictment charging Squires did make an assault on a girl of the age of twelve years against her will, did ravish and carnally know, he the said Squires being a married man having a wife alive other than the said girl. The jury returned a verdict that the defendant had carnal knowledge of the body of the said girl but without violence and not against her will. Carnal knowledge means sexual bodily connection. The verdict was a good one. 

Com. v. Holt, 121 Mass. 61

The fact that the defendant was married may be proved by his admission or by general repute. On defendant's arrest his

CRIMINAL CASES 

statement that he had a wife in England and his statement to the effect of his getting a divorce were evidence that he had a wife living; if he speaks of a woman living with him as his wife, this is evidence of cohabitation. 

Com. v. O'Connor, 107 Mass. 219

That a woman was delivered of a child which might have been begotten about the time of the alleged adultery is not admissible to show intercourse. 

Com. v. Horton, 2 Gray, 354

Upon the trial in one county with a woman named, evidence is not admissible that the defendant after the time alleged in the indictment cohabitated with the same woman in another county.

Trial. 

Com. v. Seeley, 167 Mass. 163 

Com. v. Elwell, 2 Met. 190

Two defendants indicted separately may be tried together. 

AGENCY 

See under Accomplice and Alcoholic beverages. 

Com. v. Stevens, 153 Mass. 421

An agent who sold liquor to a minor is guilty even if he thought the minor was not so, but the employer is not liable if the jury believes he honestly gave orders not to sell to minors. 

Com. v. Wachendorf, 141 Mass. 270

If the servant or agent sells liquor against the instructions of his employer, and the jury believes the instructions were given in good faith, the defendant must be acquitted. 

Com. v. O'Leary, 143 Mass. 95

An unlawful sale to an agent, with notice on the part of the seller that he is buying for his principal, must be charged as a sale to the principal. A sale to the agent of an undisclosed principal may be alleged as a sale to either the agent or the principal. 

Com. v. Ryan, 160 Mass. 172

It is no defense for one who commits a crime to show that he was acting as a servant and under the direction of his master.

CRIMINAL CASES 

19 

Farley v. Faxon, 143 Mass. 284

Where a person is a mere messenger and the sale was lawfully made, he could not be guilty of illegal transportation of the liquor sold to him, by bringing it to the purchaser, even if the latter resided in a town where such sales would lawfully be made. 

Com. v. Stevens, 155 Mass. 291

To convict the master of the acts of his servant, the commonwealth must show that the master participated in the act, countenanced or approved it. 

Com. v. Wilkins, 243 Mass. 356

A police officer is not the agent of the government when acting outside the scope of his authority. 

ALCOHOLIC BEVERAGES GEN. LAWS, ACTS OF 1933, CHAP. 376 Search warrant. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 97 Mass. 332

The phrase "the same is a place of common resort" does not conform to the statute. The statute requires "a place of common resort is kept therein." 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 4 Allen, 601

A complaint alleging that certain intoxicating liquor is kept in a specified place, etc., is not supported by a verdict which finds that the liquor was owned or kept there. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 108 Mass. 19

If an application for the search in a store, etc., an affidavit is not essential, but for a dwelling house it is. If the dwelling house and store are in the same building no affidavit is essential. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 113 Mass. 13 "If found." Form of search warrant where these words are in the complaint and warrant. If liquor is not found the implements of sale cannot be seized. The officer returned some of the liquors to the claimant before an order of notice was issued on the authority of the district attorney.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 110 Mass. 416

A certain quantity, being about and not exceeding 100 gallons, means intoxicating, inasmuch as intoxicating liquors are included in the general averment. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 105 Mass. 178

Warrants must allege that probable cause has been shown for their issue; not to do so is fatal. This, notwithstanding that the statute provides forms that may be used. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 105 Mass. 181

An affidavit by one of the complainants is sufficient for the issuance of the warrant and need not be framed with technical accuracy as it does not form a part of the complaint. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 110 Mass. 500

A complaint and warrant to search for intoxicating liquors must be specific and not too general. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 103 Mass. 448 “Received and sworn to" which is the preamble of a search warrant sets forth that the complaint was made on oath by the said two complainants and that they are of full age and competent to testify, sufficiently shows that the complainants both signed the complaint and were both sworn. 

Guenther v. Day, 6 Gray, 490

A search warrant that does not recite the names of the complainants is void. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 108 Mass. 290 

Downing v. Porter, 8 Gray, 539

Neither the jurisdiction of the court nor the validity of the proceedings for the forfeiture of the liquor is affected by the omission to summon the complainants as witness as directed. A subpoena may issue instead. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 97 Mass. 62

The phrase "that the complainants were summoned to appear as witnesses" need not appear on the complaint. Make due return is sufficient instruction. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 6 Allen, 596

A constable may serve a search warrant which is issued upon the complaint of himself and another person.

CRIMINAL CASES 

21 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 110 Mass. 182 "That intoxicating liquors were and still are kept and deposited." The jurat to the complaint need not contain an averment that probable cause has been shown for the issuance of the complaint, but the warrant must. If the affidavit contains an averment that one of the complainants has reason to believe and does believe that liquor was sold in a dwelling house within one month, it need not aver that a place of common resort is kept in the house. 

Downing v. Porter, 8 Gray, 539

A certain quantity of gin being about and not exceeding 100 gallons is a sufficient description. 

Service of warrant. 

Com. v. Martin, 105 Mass. 178

An officer who is serving a void warrant can be ejected from the premises; that is, if only legal force is used. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 6 Allen, 596

A constable may serve a warrant which has been issued upon the complaint of himself and another person. 

Kent v. Willey, 11 Gray, 368

An officer who seizes property by authority of law must show that he has done all that is required of him in order to complete and fulfill the duty imposed upon him; otherwise he is a trespasser ab initio. 

Arthur v. Flanders, 10 Gray, 107

An officer seizing liquor not mentioned in the warrant is liable to the owner. 

Com. v. Hinds, 145 Mass. 182

A warrant to seize intoxicating liquor may be executed in the night time. 

Blackman v. Nickerson, 188 Mass. 399

The officer in searching for liquor took away a safe and had it opened. No liquor was found therein. He returned the safe. 

Held guilty of conversion. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 203 Mass. 585

The officer seized more liquors than the warrant called for; the court can forfeit all the liquors that were seized lawfully.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Courtney, 243 Mass. 363

The officer seized articles not included in the warrant. The seizure was admissible as to the articles taken lawfully, and not legal to the articles taken illegally. Evidence is admissible no matter how obtained. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 97 Mass. 62 "And make due return of the warrant" is sufficient. 

Allen v. Staples, 72 Mass. 491 

A search warrant process is constitutional. 

Com. v. Blood, 77 Mass. 74

One with a search warrant was asked what he did while in the house. The court allowed him to testify even though the defendant was not present at the search. 

Com. v. Keenan, 148 Mass. 470

An officer can testify to what he found by virtue of the warrant and it is admissible without proof of the production or the existence of the same. 

Com. v. McCue, 121 Mass. 358

An officer can testify to what he found on the warrant who makes the seizure and makes a return thereon. 

Wright v. Dressel, 140 Mass. 147

A warrant can be served on the Lord's day. It is not a civil process. 

Liquors already seized. 

Allen v. Staples, 72 Mass. 491

A warrant may be issued to seize liquors already seized for illegal transportation and the warrant need not state that fact. 

It need not name the owner, if naming the person in whose possession the same was found. Owner cannot replevin liquor in the possession of the officer. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 107 Mass. 386

A warrant to search a vehicle need not particularly describe it. The complaint to seize liquor already seized, dates back to the time of the seizure, and is not vitiated by the averment that they are still in the possession of the person from whom seized.

CRIMINAL CASES 

23 

Illegal warrant. 

Com. v. Koslowsky, 243 Mass. 538

It is immaterial whether an officer searching the defendant's house and finding liquor there had a warrant to search or not, or whether or not it was defective, as evidence pertinent to the issue is admissible though procured in an irregular or illegal manner. 

Com. v. Henderson, 140 Mass. 303

It is immaterial whether the proceedings of the officer were regular and lawful or not, in the service of the warrant. 

Com. v. Wilkins, 243 Mass. 356 

The 4th amendment of the Constitution does not apply to the courts of this commonwealth. A police officer seized liquor from the pocket of the defendant. No matter how evidence is obtained, it is admissible. 

Com. v. Tibbets, 157 Mass. 519

The officers had a warrant for liquor and seized abortion implements. The evidence obtained was admissible. 

Com. v. Brelsford, 161 Mass. 61

The fact that the liquor was illegally seized does not render the admission of evidence inadmissible. 

Com. v. Courtney, 243 Mass. 363

An officer in making a search for liquor did acts not authorized by the warrant, as destroying and seizing property other than liquor did not taint with extraneous illegality all that was done, and his acts were illegal only to the extent that they were not authorized by the warrant. 

Com. v. Newton, 123 Mass. 420

An officer acting on a warrant who entered a building described as occupied by A, but in fact occupied by A and B in separate tenements, and seized liquor kept by A has no right to enter and search a closet which is part of B's tenement and is not used by A as a place for keeping liquor, although he believes that the closet is in A's occupation, and B may resist his entry, using reasonable force to do so.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Description of the premises to be searched. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 140 Mass. 287

The outbuildings and the curtilage thereof do not include a building not in the same enclosure but only connected with a narrow passageway, each building having a separate entrance. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 122 Mass. 36

Where a building is numbered by one or more numbers any one will be sufficient if the premises are as well known by one number as another. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 122 Mass. 8

One who has a store on the first floor and three upper floors used as a dwelling house and connected with an independent stairway can be described as having a tenement. The place was not a dwelling house, the upper floors were not material. 

Com. v. McDonald, 160 Mass. 528

The fact that one building is numbered on one street is not conclusive that it is the same as on the other; two streets that are contiguous although different names are the same street. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 150 Mass. 164 "A certain tenement situated on E. street and numbered 136 on said street" sufficiently describes the premises. 

Com. v. Kane, 173 Mass. 477

A room designated as suite two in the second story of a certain building is a sufficient description. 

Com. v. Reed, 162 Mass. 215

The person believed to be the owner must be set out by special designation. On a trial against the liquors the commonwealth cannot prevail unless kept by the person named in the complaint. 

Downing v. Porter, 74 Mass. 539

A warrant is not invalidated by misnaming one street if in other respects it describes the identity of the place. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 122 Mass. 14

A warrant which describes the search in the cellar of the house used for the storage of liquor is sufficient notwithstanding the fact that the cellar was of the dwelling house.

CRIMINAL CASES 

25 

Com. v. Cogan, 107 Mass. 212

One room used as a shop may be described as a tenement. 

Com. v. Fraher, 126 Mass. 56

A tenement may consist of two rooms. 

Clark v. Fontaine, 144 Mass. 287

A part of a room may be a tenement. 

Com. v. Patterson, 153 Mass. 5

A lot of land with detached buildings used together constitutes a tenement. 

Com. v. Purcell, 154 Mass. 388

A hotel is a place within the meaning of the nuisance statute as to all buildings, places and tenements. 

Com. v. Mullen, 166 Mass. 377

A building occupied as a dwelling house, whether attached to the land or not, is a tenement. 

Com. v. Dana, 43 Mass. 329

A place to be searched as the office of D and the number thereof, and the street, although occupied by A in connection with D, is a sufficient description. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 108 Mass. 19 

May issue for a store in a dwelling house. 

Com. v. Leddy, 105 Mass. 381 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 122 Mass. 14

An averment that a dwelling house is a place of common resort is sufficient. 

Allen v. Staples, 72 Mass. 491

Need not name the owner of the liquor if it names the person in whose possession it is found. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 108 Mass. 19

In an application for the search of a store, an affidavit is not essential but for a dwelling house it is. If the dwelling house and the store are in the same building no affidavit is necessary. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 113 Mass. 208

The name of a street which is known by the name that it has gone under for a long space of time is a sufficient designation, even if that is not the name that the city has given it.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 109 Mass. 371, 373

An averment in a warrant to search the cellar and the first floor of a building and the back room over the rear part of the store in the building and prayed for a warrant to search the building above described is void as it covered too much ground. 

Com. v. Leddy, 105 Mass. 381

A warrant was issued to search the house of one Gilligan. 

The house was occupied by Gilligan and one Leddy, each occupying one half of the cellar, it being divided by a partition and a common passageway which was locked. In Leddy's part a barrel of ale was horsed up with a faucet leading into Gilligan's part. 

This was legally seized as it was in the possession of Gilligan. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 6 Allen, 596

A warrant described a building as No. 22. Although it was actually numbered No. 27, the directory of the city gave it as No. 

22. The jury was instructed that if it was as well known by one number as another the description was sufficient. 

Proceedings after seizure-forfeiture. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 172 Mass. 311 

Forfeiture is not an unusual punishment. 

Com. v. Doyle, 132 Mass. 244

In proceedings for the seizure and forfeiture of liquors the time alleged is material and must be proved as alleged; that is, it must be proved that at the time of the seizure of the liquors they were kept by the person alleged with the intent to sell them unlawfully. 

Fitzwilliam v. Com., 258 Mass. 103

It is the essence of a proceeding in rem that jurisdiction is secured by the power of the court over the res, and that without running counter to the provisions of the Federal Constitution or the Constitution of the Commonwealth, the proceedings may be instituted and carried to judgment although there has been no personal service upon a claimant within the state or notice to name by those outside it. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 138 Mass. 506

The complaint must set forth fully and formally the offense which the claimant has committed and which works the forfeiture of the liquor so that he may know what specific charge he is to meet.

CRIMINAL CASES 

27 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 142 Mass. 170 

Where one of the complainants signed by initial F. W. Thayer and was sworn the complaint is good. Need not on the forfeiture proceedings show that the liquor was sold within the month; it is enough if liquors were being illegally kept at the time of the issuance of the warrant. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 218 Mass. 602

The allegations must as in other criminal cases be proved beyond a reasonable doubt. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 107 Mass. 396

Liquors kept for sale may be forfeited as a nuisance, although their owner is innocent of the illegal purpose of the bailee; the principle is analogous to that of the seizure of a piratical or smuggling vessel. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 113 Mass. 23

On forfeiture although the claimant fails to appear on an appeal to the Superior Court the court cannot make an order forfeiting the liquors unless the allegations are proved as in every criminal case.

Order of Notice. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 129 Mass. 172

An order of notice stating that the warrant was issued by a district court whereas in fact it was issued by a special justice does not invalidate the proceedings. 

Voessch v. Phelps, 112 Mass. 407

Where an order of notice has not been served after a seizure this will not make the prior act of the seizure a trespass. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 4 Allen, 593

The issuance of an order of notice is merely a ministerial act and need not be recorded. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 97 Mass. 601

The 24 hours in which an order of notice shall issue are exclusive of the Lord's day. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 110 Mass. 182

A claimant cannot dispute the regularity of the order of notice, if in fact he received notice and appeared generally.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Evidence introduced on a search warrant. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 116 Mass. 342

A search warrant must clearly indicate the place to be searched. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 115 Mass. 145

A warrant which authorizes the search of any part of a building not included in the description in the complaint is void. The complaint alleged the basement and three stories, the warrant stated all outhouses and sheds. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 117 Mass. 427

Warrant was for 1952 Washington Street, in the basement; liquor was found in the basement of 195. Not a variance. It is for the jury to say whether the liquors seized in the room under the sidewalk opening into the basement were in part of the basement. 

Com. v. Lyden, 113 Mass. 452

A question as to what an officer swore to on an application for a search warrant can only be asked by the production of the original or of an attested copy. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 116 Mass. 26

In a search complaint an allegation that the liquors were kept by an unknown person is sufficient. Liquors stored in freighthouse subject to shipment are not intended for sale as required by law; they are not kept or deposited for sale. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 103 Mass. 454

When the proceedings on the forfeiture of liquor are quashed the court must issue an order returning them to the owner or the place from which they were taken. 

Com. v. Welch, 110 Mass. 359

Liquors found under the floor held to be found in the tenement. 

Harlan v. Seagrave, 77 Mass. 207

Need not allege in the complaint that probable cause has been shown for the issuance of the same. It must be inserted in the warrant. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 150 Mass. 164

Pool tables were seized on a warrant for intoxicating liquors as implements of sale. The defendant did not have any license

CRIMINAL CASES 

29 to keep pool tables for gain. No evidence was offered that they were used in the selling of liquor. Forfeited. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 130 Mass. 29

A warrant issued by a trial justice to an officer in which he is directed to make return of this warrant . . . does not authorize him to return it to another justice. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 13 Allen, 52

An affidavit for the issuance of a warrant may be issued on information given to the applicant. When two sign it, hearsay is admissible as to one of them. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 138 Mass. 506

The complaint must set forth fully and formally the offense which the claimant has committed so that he may know what he has to meet. 

Com. v. Tucker, 189 Mass. 457

Evidence obtained under a warrant is admissible in evidence. 

Downing v. Porter, 74 Mass. 539 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 95 Mass. 561

The statement in a warrant "being about and not exceeding the amount of the liquor expected to be seized” is legal. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 122 Mass. 14

It is a good warrant if only one of the complainants is sworn to its contents. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 80 Mass. 375

A complaint for the seizure of liquors is a criminal complaint 

Equity will not enjoin a search. 

Harmon v. Police Commissioner, 274 Mass. 56

Equity will not interfere to enjoin the acts of the police unless it appears the statute is unconstitutional. In above case the officers exceeded their authority; officers were stationed outside the door of the suspected place, numerous warrants were served, 36 were legally issued and served on the premises, 34 without process. No liquor was seized. Writ refused.

Liquors seized in a vehicle - Automobile. 

Allen v. Staples, 6 Gray, 491

A warrant may issue to seize liquors already seized for illegal transportation and the warrant need not state that fact.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Need not name the owner if naming the person in whose possession the same was found. Owner cannot replevin the liquor from the officer. 

Fitzwilliam v. Com., 258. Mass. 103 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 258 Mass. 85

An automobile must be a container of liquor or an implement of sale and must be so stated in the officer's return. A sold an automobile to B on a conditional contract of sale. C, who was driving, was arrested for illegal transportation, and the automobile was forfeited. The innocence of the seller does not entitle him to the automobile. 

Com. v. Automobile, 261 Mass. 504

Car sold with a lease to A. A registers it in the name of B. 

B is arrested for illegal transportation and the car is forfeited. 

The vendor had no guilty knowledge that the car was to be illegally used. It was not depriving the owner of property without due process of law. Whether the right to enforce forfeiture would exist when the property was stolen was not decided. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 107 Mass. 386

A warrant to search a vehicle need not particularly describe it. The complaint to seize liquor already seized dates back to the time of the seizure and is not vitiated by an averment that they are still in his possession. 

State of Maine v. Roach, 74 Maine, 562

An automobile is not a place within the meaning of a fixed place. Constantly changing its position, does not come within the above meaning. The contents are not kept or deposited, but were being carried for the purpose of afterwards being deposited and kept in some place. 

Jones v. Root, 72 Mass. 435

Officers seizing liquor being illegally transported can hold the vehicle until the liquor can be removed from the wagon. 

Evidence. 

Com. v. McLaughlin, 108 Mass. 477

It is competent to show that the liquor was sold at two places where liquor was kept by the defendant.

CRIMINAL CASES 

31 

Com. v. Kenney, 115 Mass. 149

Evidence that the defendant delivered liquor four months previous to the same house is competent, if there is no proof of changed ownership. 

Com. v. Waters, 77 Mass. 81

From place to place within the commonwealth means in the same town, cannot introduce evidence that the person to whom he was delivering them was tried and convicted of an illegal sale. 

Mason v. Lothrop, 7 Gray, 354

In order to justify an arrest for illegal transportation without a warrant the officer must have reasonable proof; belief and cause to believe are not enough. 

Com. v. Currier, 164 Mass. 544

Transporting liquor in a no-license town, one assisting the driver is guilty if he knowingly aided and assisted. 

Com. v. Fostello, 208 Mass. 547

The law requiring an expressman to have a license to carry liquor into a no-license town, he shall at all times carry in his wagon a book in which the entries are made. 

Com. v. Beck, 187 Mass. 15 

Does not apply to individuals not conducting an express business. 

Com. v. Radocchia, 205 Mass. 455

A dealer in liquor can deliver them to the purchaser either by himself or by his servant, if he does it not for hire or reward, and it is a question of fact for the jury whether the hire or reward is included in the price of the liquors. 

Com. v. Shea, 185 Mass. 89

The expressman who brings liquor into a no-license town, if he does not enter on his books a description, etc., is guilty of illegal transportation. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 253 Mass. 581

The jury was asked on the forfeiture of an automobile whether the truck seized was used or kept or provided to be used as an implement of sale, or as a container of liquor in the illegal keeping for sale of liquor, and the jury answered in the affirmative, hence the car was forfeited.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 258 Mass. 85 

In this case the allegation in the complaint is that the liquors were in four metallic containers. The return on the warrant did not state that the vehicle was an implement of sale, or furniture if kept or provided to be used in the illegal sale of such liquor. There must be evidence of intent to sell contrary to law. 

Simple transportation by itself alone is not proof of such intent. 

Com. v. Dzwaicin, 252 Mass. 126

The Baby Volstead act is constitutional. The commonwealth need not prove that the defendant knew that he was transporting liquor that was intoxicating, and that it was to be used for beverage purposes, nor that the transportation by the defendant was for an unlawful purpose. The doing of the act constitutes the crime and the intent is not necessary. See Chapter 138, Section 75, and amendments. 

Com. v. Kowalsky, 243 Mass. 538

If the whisky found in the house of the defendant was kept with intent to sell contrary to law, it would be immaterial that it was not intended to be sold in the place where it was found. 

If kept in the defendant's house with the intent by him to sell it at his place of business, he is guilty of the offense charged. 

Lower court can bind over case to Federal Court. 

Goulis v. Justices, 246 Mass. 1

A justice of a district court can bind over a case to the Federal Court. The justice rescinded a previous order for the return of the liquors seized and ordered them to be sent to the Federal 

Court for disposition. Constitutionality. 

Com. v. Nickerson, 236 Mass. 281

The lower courts and the courts of the commonwealth have jurisdiction of all liquor cases. 

Jones v. Town of Weston, 238 Mass. 218

The liquor law of the commonwealth - that is, Chapter 138 of the General Laws and amendments- does not conflict with the 18th amendment. 

Com. v. Petranitch, 183 Mass. 217 

Wines made in New York are not native wines. Chapter 100,

Section 1 of the Rev. Laws is unconstitutional. Part of a statute which is unconstitutional does not affect the rest of the statute.

CRIMINAL CASES 

333 

Nonintoxicating. 

Jones v. Town of Weston, 238 Mass. 218

No Massachusetts law can authorize the sale of nonintoxicating liquors, prohibited by the Federal Laws. Now under the Acts of 1932, Chapter 120, this has been changed. 

Spirituous and intoxicating. 

Com. v. Herrick, 60 Mass. 465 

Com. v. Livermore, 4 Gray, 18 

Com. v. Markoe, 17 Pick. 465 "Spirituous" and "intoxicating" are not synonymous. All spirituous liquor is intoxicating, yet all intoxicating liquor is not spirituous. Spirituous liquor means distilled liquor, and such, we believe, is the meaning of the statute. Distilled liquor or spirits is obtained by distillation from wine or other fermented fruit or from starchy materials as corn, rye, barley or potatoes which first must be brewed. Distilled liquor cannot be manufactured until it has passed through the process of distillation. 

Com. v. Curran, 119 Mass. 206 

Distilled spirits is whisky. 

Com. v. Nickerson, 236 Mass. 281 

Alcohol, brandy, whisky, rum and gin of the United States are included within the phrase "distilled spirits" under the laws of Massachusetts. 

Mills v. Perkins, 120 Mass. 41

Alcohol, like any other intoxicating liquor, may be used medicinally, and when so used may be properly described as a drug. 

Com. v. Blos, 116 Mass. 56

The fact that a substance contains alcohol does not mean that it is spirituous within the meaning of the statute; the question as to whether or not it was intoxicating should be submitted to the jury. 

Com. v. Timothy, 8 Gray, 480

The statute declares certain liquor to be intoxicating, and if the liquor sold was not in fact intoxicating, still, if it was within that section of the statute, the keeping of it with the intent to sell was by the terms of the statute to be punished as if it was intoxicating.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Beverage. 

Com. v. Pease, 110 Mass. 412

The defendant was charged with keeping and exposing sweet bitters. This was a well-known medicine and the defendant was entitled to know the percentage of alcohol from the witnesses for the commonwealth. The commonwealth having introduced evidence that they were intoxicating, the defendant was entitled to show the contrary. 

Com. v. Lanides, 239 Mass. 103 

The defendant sold jamaica ginger. The intent of the defendant in making the sale is immaterial. If the article in reality sold is intoxicating liquor it is illegal, even if sold as a medicine. 

The offense is not the intention, it is the act. 

Com. v. Ramsdell, 130 Mass. 68

If the article sold cannot be used as an intoxicating liquor it is not within the prohibition of the statute, although it contains as one of its ingredients some spirituous liquor. 

Com. v. Brennan, 262 Mass. 181

The defendant sold claret punch concentrate, containing more than 234% of alcohol. The fact that it contained more than the illegal percentage does not make it intoxicating, under the statute. 

Question: Did the contents of the bottle contain a substance which was a beverage? The advertisement on the bottle was taken to be construed that it was a beverage. The fact that the commonwealth failed to show that it caused intoxication is not a defense. The fact that the defendant had a Federal Permit of 

8% is not a defense. If it was sold in violation of the state law, defendant is guilty. Not competent to show that the defendant acted in good faith. 

Com. v. Ambrozievitz, 269 Mass. 97

The complaint was for exposing and keeping. The officers found a still and five barrels of bubbling mash. The still was not in operation at the time. There was not any evidence that the defendant had ever used it. No analysis was made. The court decided that it was not a beverage. 

Expert opinion - Presence of alcohol. 

Com. v. O'Donnell, 143 Mass. 178

The complaint was for exposing and keeping. The defendant admitted selling ale and introduced evidence that it was under

CRIMINAL CASES 

35 the legal per cent. The commonwealth offered evidence that on two different occasions men were seen going in sober and coming out drunk, to show that the liquor was above the legal per cent and was intoxicating. 

Com. v. Matthews, 129 Mass. 485

A witness cannot testify that certain beer contained 32% of alcohol. It is hearsay unless he spoke of actual knowledge. 

Com. v. Collier, 134 Mass. 203

A witness testified that he tasted liquor sold to him by the defendant and that it was beer and he could tell by the taste alone whether it contained 3% of alcohol. 

Com. v. Nickerson, 236 Mass. 281 

Com. v. Timothy, 8 Gray, 480

It is a matter of common knowledge that whisky is intoxicating. 

Com. v. Moinehan, 140 Mass. 463 

Com. v. D'Amico, 254 Mass. 512

Witnesses can testify to what liquor is by knowledge or inference perceived by the senses. 

Com. v. Snow, 133 Mass. 575

At a trial for exposing and keeping it is sufficient for the commonwealth to prove that the defendant kept lager beer with the intent to sell the same unlawfully, without further proof that it was intoxicating, or that it contained more than the legal per cent of alcohol. 

Com. v. Morgan, 149 Mass. 314

It is common knowledge that alcohol is the intoxicating element in intoxicating liquor; whisky is alcohol and water and other elements of which alcohol alone is intoxicating; mixed liquor is distilled spirits which are used as a beverage containing alcohol mingled with water and other substances. Cider. 

Com. v. McGrath, 185 Mass. 1 

Com. v. Chapel, 116 Mass. 7

One who keeps cider without a license is guilty of exposing and keeping and the commonwealth need not prove the percentage of alcohol.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Boyden, 183 Mass. 1

Farmers and manufacturers cannot sell cider without a license if it is more than 3% alcohol. See Acts 189, Chapter 489. 

Com. v. Greenwood, 205 Mass. 124 

One who manufactures may sell at wholesale. Sales made in large quantities are wholesale. A sale of a quart of whisky is not wholesale. 

Com. v. Mahoney, 152 Mass. 493

A sale of cider by the maker through his servant or agent hired by him for the purpose is within the exceptions, although the cider is not made by the defendant and he does not raise all the apples from which it is made. 

Purchaser of liquor. 

Com. v. Willard, 39 Mass. 476 

Com. v. Kimball, 24 Pick. 366

The purchaser of intoxicating liquor sold in violation of law does not under the ordinary circumstances subject himself to any penalty, either at common law, as inducing the seller to commit a misdemeanor, or under the statute.

Evidence obtained on an illegal search is admissible. 

Com. v. Wilkins, 243 Mass. 356 

Com. v. Dana, 2 Met. 329 

The 4th amendment to the Constitution does not apply to the courts of this commonwealth. A police officer seized liquor from the pocket of the defendant. The court said no matter how the evidence is obtained it is still admissible in evidence. 

Com. v. Brelsford, 161 Mass. 61

The fact that liquor was taken illegally does not render the evidence inadmissible. 

Com. v. Murphy, 155 Mass. 284

The fact that it was procured to be used as evidence will not prohibit a conviction.

Illegal testimony. 

Com. v. Hurley, 158 Mass. 159

A police officer may testify to material facts within his knowledge, although he was acting unlawfully when the facts came into his possession.

CRIMINAL CASES 

37 

Analysis. 

A certificate of the analysis of the seized liquor signed by the analyst is admissible. 

Com. v. Stoler, 259 Mass. 109

Although the certificate requires the name and address of the one seizing the liquor, it is not voided by the fact that a sergeant of police to whom it was turned over by the person actually seizing it, signed it. 

Com. v. Kendrick, 147 Mass. 444

Beer was sent in a carton to the state assayer to be analyzed and marked. The evidence did not disclose whether the beer or the box was marked. A question for the jury whether the beer was the same beer seized. 

Second offense. 

Com. v. Marchand, 155 Mass. 8

When the first offense was committed the penalty was fine or imprisonment. When the second offense was committed the penalty was fine and imprisonment. The court had the right to sentence to fine and imprisonment. 

Com. v. Makarian, 250 Mass. 211

After alleging the offense in the complaint the pleader alleged a second offense. This if untrue can be rejected as surplusage. 

Com. v. Fontain, 127 Mass. 452

The offense for keeping a tenement for the illegal sale and keeping of liquor, which makes it a nuisance, is a distinct and different offense from that of unlawfully keeping liquor for sale. 

A conviction or acquittal of either is no bar to an indictment for the other even upon the same evidence. Knowledge. 

Com. v. Hallett, 103 Mass. 452 

Com. v. Boynton, 2 Allen, 160 

Com. v. Anthes, 12 Gray, 29

It is no defense to a seller of liquor that he believed what he sold was a medicine or that he did not know it was intoxicating.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Minor.

Hamer v. Eldredge, 171 Mass. 250 

Giving and selling liquors to a minor are the same. Dump. 

Com. v. O'Connor, 11 Gray, 94

A drainer or dump, so called, is an unusual implement in the sale of liquor and may be testified to as to its use. Manufacture. 

Com. v. Green, 253 Mass. 458 

The defendant brewed beer more than 234% of alcohol.

Manufacture means make. The fact that it is for the house does not excuse the defendant. The intent is not essential. The statute makes no difference between manufactured for home use and consumption, and manufacturing for sale. Now repealed. 

Com. v. Ambrozievitz, 269 Mass. 97 

Officer found moonshine in a barrel. The defendant could not be convicted of manufacturing because the mash was not intoxicating liquor, as it had not been distilled. The court cannot take judicial notice that mash is a beverage or intoxicating. Club. 

Com. v. Pomphret, 137 Mass. 564

Members pay an admission fee on joining. No outsiders own any liquor. The club employs a steward who dispenses the liquor to members who pay for it with checks representing sums, which checks were purchased from the steward. The money received from the sales is used for the purpose of purchasing liquors. 

Not guilty of exposing and keeping. 

Com. v. Woelz, 219 Mass. 37

A steward who sold liquors to members of an incorporated club having no license was guilty. No defense that he was an employee. 

Com. v. Ryan, 152 Mass. 283

Persons had liquor in their lockers. Fixtures resembling a bar room, many men found drinking out of a bottle not bearing the names of the members. Merely a subterfuge and guilty of exposing and keeping.

CRIMINAL CASES 

39 

Com. v. Smith, 102 Mass. 144

The members of a club jointly contributed money to purchase of liquors by the steward. The steward issued checks to members who purchased the liquor. If the jury finds that the liquor belonged to the members, that the steward merely kept the liquor, he was not guilty; if the jury finds this to be an evasion then he is guilty.

Illegal consideration. 

Brown v. Perkins, 12 Gray, 89

Intoxicating liquors are not a nuisance, they are properly entitled to protection as property, they may be the subject of larceny, and no man can be deprived of them without due process of law, including trial by jury. 

Richards v. Woodward, 113 Mass. 285

An importer who sells liquor in the original package, and although he knows that the purchaser intends to and will resell it in violation of law, can recover for the purchase price. 

Orcutt v. Symonds, 107 Mass. 382

A buyer of intoxicating liquor sold in violation of law may maintain an action to recover back his judgment for them, although he bought them for the purpose of selling them again unlawfully. 

Ingalls v. Baker, 13 Allen, 449

Intoxicating liquors are not liable to be seized on an execution under the statutes of this commonwealth. 

Illegal transportation. 

What is a tenement? 

See under Search warrant. 

Com. v. Fraher, 126 Mass. 56

A tenement may be so called if two rooms were used together. 

Com. v. Buckley, 147 Mass. 581

A tenement might be a single room or contiguous rooms. The defendant kept the second story and one room connected with a dumb waiter to serve liquor on the first floor. Guilty. 

Com. v. Clynes, 150 Mass. 71

Defendant had a shop on the first floor and lived upstairs. 

He sold liquor in both. Held that the different uses in both

CRIMINAL CASES 

tenements did not require a different warrant for each tenement. 

Com. v. Patterson, 153 Mass. 5

A lot of land with detached buildings divided into several tenements but not subdivided, if kept and used for liquor selling constitutes a single nuisance and can be pleaded as one tenement. 

Com. v. Mullen, 166 Mass. 377 "A building occupied by the defendant as a dwelling house" is the modern use of the word "tenement.' 

Com. v. Hersey, 144 Mass. 297

Where two persons occupy the same room, one on one side and one on the other, one can be convicted of his part if it is used for exposing and keeping of liquor. Tenement means place occupied and lived in. Where a line of two towns ran through the room of the defendant and liquor was found in part, he could be charged with a nuisance, as a nuisance is local in its nature, and must be proved as laid. 

Jurisdiction. 

Lindsey v. Stone, 123 Mass. 332

Where a purchaser in Massachusetts purchases liquor from one in Connecticut to be sold illegally in Massachusetts, the Connecticut man can recover, unless it can be shown that he has actual knowledge of the illegality. Reasonable cause is not enough. 

Com. v. Broadbeck, 124 Mass. 319

A defendant in Boston holding a license to sell liquor delivered liquor in Dedham, a no-license town. Jurisdiction is in Dedham. 

Com. v. Murray, 144 Mass. 170

Under the public statutes and acts in amendment thereof the Municipal Court has jurisdiction of violations of the liquor law. 

Com. v. Downey, 145 Mass. 377

One part of the building was in Cambridge and one part 'in Somerville. Evidence of the exposing and keeping in Somerville can be used in trying the Cambridge part.

CRIMINAL CASES 

41 

Goulis v. Stone, 246 Mass. 1 

A Federal agent can make application for process under the laws of Massachusetts for the apprehension of a violator of the Volstead Act under Section 25, title 2. A district court may (discretionary) bind a defendant over for his appearance before the 

United States Court under Section 1014 of the U. S. statutes. 

Com. v. Eggleston, 128 Mass. 408

An employee of another in Rhode Island receives orders for liquor from one in Massachusetts and under authority from his employer to receive or reject orders, accepts the order. He may be indicted for an unlawful sale in Massachusetts if the liquors are, in pursuance of his orders, delivered to the buyer in Massachusetts. 

Com. Int. Liq., 255 Mass. 515

A justice of the lower court while sitting in the Superior Court under the Acts of 1923, Chapter 469, cannot try a forfeiture of liquors where the value of the seized liquors exceeds the value of $1000. 

Com. v. Thornliey, 88 Mass. 445

Licenses granted by the United States do not permit the licensee to violate the laws of Massachusetts. 

Com. v. Dean, 110 Mass. 357

The legislature can delegate to a city the right to pass laws relative to the selling of liquor. 

Com. v. Kern, 147 Mass. 595

The crime of exposing and keeping for sale intoxicating liquors is not local in its nature and it is sufficient if the proof shows that the crime was committed in the county. 

Evidence of a time other than averred in the complaint. Selling. 

Com. v. Kelley, 10 Cush. 69

A charge for a sale on March 1 is supported by proof of a sale on March 17.

Com. v. Page, 6 Gray, 361 

Witness called by the defendant testified that since May 12 (the alleged date of the sale) he had seen liquors in the shop. Inadmissible.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Carroll, 15 Gray, 409 

Two sales, one on June 1 and one on August 1, to two men.

If the jury is satisfied that the defendant had in or about the two months mentioned made a sale to them he is guilty. 

Com. v. Hogan, 11 Gray, 312

Evidence of selling two years previous to the alleged date in the complaint is admissible. 

Com. v. Mahoney, 134 Mass. 220

The identity of the offense is not to be determined solely by the date named in the complaint or by that given in evidence. 

Com. v. McNeff, 145 Mass. 406. 

Com. v. Haher, 113 Mass. 207

Sales made before the date of the complaint may be admitted upon the question as to whether or not the defendant kept the tenement. 

Com. v. Brothers, 158 Mass. 200

The commonwealth can introduce evidence that at other times near the date, persons were seen going in and coming out of the place under circumstances which the jury might deem suspicious. 

Com. v. Shea, 160 Mass. 6 

Evidence of a sale made on August 25 not too remote. Liquor was found on a search Oct. 2. A traveled path led from the house in which the liquor was found to a saloon of the defendant. 

Com. v. Galligan, 156 Mass. 270

A sale of liquor after the day named in the complaint is not too remote. 

Com. v. Kelley, 64 Mass. 69 

Date of the alleged sale was on the first day of the month.

Evidence of a sale on the 17th is admissible. 

Com. v. Elwell, 67 Mass. 463

A complaint for being a common seller on a day certain. Evidence of sales prior to and subsequent are not admissible; the complaint should have been pleaded continuando.

CRIMINAL CASES 

43 

Exposing and keeping. 

Com. v. Carr, 111 Mass. 423 

The complaint charged on a day certain, June 29. Several days prior the defendant sold liquors. The time is not essential and this evidence is admissible. 

Com. v. Dearbon, 109 Mass. 368

An admission that five months prior to the date of the complaint, July 1871, he was the owner of the shop has a tendency to show that he is still the owner. He was seen in the shop during that time. Evidence of sale prior to the date is competent as tending to show what use the defendant made of the shop. 

Com. v. Maloney, 112 Mass. 283

Defendant was charged with exposing and keeping liquors on Sept. 10. On the appeal the evidence was confined to that date. 

It is true that in criminal prosecutions the exact date need not be necessarily proved as laid, but every complaint must allege a precise date. In the above case on that day there was one punishment but later the law was changed, hence the date in the complaint in this class of cases must be proved as laid. 

Com. v. Berry, 109 Mass. 366

Complaint alleged January 12. The next week liquor was found in the place and in the interim men were seen leaving the place. Admissible. 

Com. v. Stoehr, 109 Mass. 365

Defendant was charged with exposing and keeping on January 22. Evidence could be introduced of liquor seized in the same place three weeks before that date and of sales made after the seizure, as tending to show that the liquors found on the premises on the day named in the complaint were kept by the defendant with intent to sell. 

Com. v. Doyle, 132 Mass. 244

In a complaint for exposing and keeping the allegation of the time is immaterial and need not be proved, it being enough to prove that the offense was committed at any time within the statute of limitations. The complaint charged a day certain. 

Com. v. Hurley, 158 Mass. 159 

Com. v. Dillane, 1 Gray, 483

Complaint alleged on a day certain; the commonwealth can introduce evidence of violations 30 days previous.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Neylon, 159 Mass. 541 

Complaint alleged violations from July 18 to August 19.

Evidence is admissible on August 30 and October 19 as not being too remote. 

Com. v. Matthews, 129 Mass. 487

Upon the question of intent the acts of the defendant sometime after as well as shortly before are admissible. The keeping might be inferred and the commonwealth might prove a continuous keeping in ten days after the date of the complaint. 

Com. v. Gagne, 153 Mass. 205

Two counts, one charging January 23 and one January 25. The commonwealth can introduce evidence of violations seven months previous. Not too remote. 

Com. v. Patalone, 254 Mass. 69

A raid was made four days after the date in the indictment; implements, etc., were seized and the jury may well have inferred from their nature that they or some of them were on the premises before the last date in the indictment. 

Com. v. Carney, 152 Mass. 566 

The complaint alleges that from May 1 to May 18 the defendant exposed and kept liquors. Evidence on the 18th is admissible. 

Com. v. Vincent, 165 Mass. 18

Evidence within eight weeks of the day on which the offense is charged is admissible to show the intent on the day charged. After the day evidence is also admissible. 

Com. v. Kyne, 162 Mass. 146
