A statement made to a United States marshal by the defendant four months after, regarding liquor on the premises, is admissible. 

Com. v. Henderson, 140 Mass. 303

Evidence made two days previous to the date in the complaint is admissible. 

Com. v. Finnerty, 148 Mass. 162

This is a continuing offense which extends over a long or short period of time, and evidence can be introduced three weeks after the date in the complaint.

CRIMINAL CASES 

45 

Com. v. Powers, 123 Mass. 244

Evidence of the condition of the room at eight o'clock a.m. of the day after the date of the complaint as to appointment and fixtures is admissible, if connected with other evidence connecting the defendant with the crime charged. 

Com. v. Carney, 108 Mass. 417

The allegation was from June 1 to October 1, 1871. Evidence was introduced that July 25, July 3, and September 23, 1870, the defendant was the keeper of the place. This is admissible as tending to show that in the absence of any apparent change the defendant continued occupancy of the place. 

Agent and principal. 

Com. v. Fowler, 145 Mass. 398

A complaint for selling liquor to A is maintained by proof that A was acting as the agent of an undisclosed principal. A met the defendant on the street and paid for six bottles of ale and the defendant delivered them to A's house. A did not inform the defendant that he was purchasing the liquor for his father. A verdict of guilty of a sale and delivery to A. 

Com. v. Marino, 261 Mass. 460

The defendant was away from the premises. If the agent of the defendant with the consent and knowledge of the defendant made sales, the defendant is guilty of selling. 

Com. v. Finnegan, 124 Mass. 324

If one acting as agent for another purchases liquor and the complaint alleges a sale to the agent, then the defendant must be acquitted. 

Com. v. Wachendorf, 141 Mass. 270

If the servant or agent sells liquor against the instructions of his employer and the jury believes that the order was given in good faith, the defendant must be acquitted. 

Com. v. Cotton, 138 Mass. 500

Proof of a delivery is prima facie evidence of a sale. To meet this, in the case of the delivery of the goods of a principal by his agent it is proper to allow the agent to show that he had authority from his principal to give them away.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Williams, 4 Allen, 587

If the defendant made an actual sale of the liquor whether on his own account or assuming to act as agent for another, and whether the liquor belonged to him or not, and whether he had or had not any authority from another, he would be responsible. 

But, on the other hand, if the defendant merely informed some third person that someone desired to buy some liquor and was supplied by such third person with the liquor to deliver, and received of the buyer the money to give to the seller so as in effect to be a messenger between the buyer and the seller, the defendant would not be criminally responsible. 

Falvey v. Faxon, 143 Mass. 284

Where a person is a mere messenger and the sale was lawfully made, he would not be guilty of illegal transportation of liquor sold by bringing it to the purchaser, even if the latter resided in a town where sales could not lawfully be made. 

Com. v. Verry, 12 Gray, 124 

Com. v. McGuire, 11 Gray, 460

A sale to the agent of an undisclosed principal may be alleged as a sale to the agent. 

Com. v. Ryan, 160 Mass. 172

It is no defense to one who commits a crime to show that he was acting as a servant and under the direction of a master. 

Com. v. Putnam, 4 Gray, 16

In order to convict an employer for a sale by his clerk the jury must find and be satisfied of his assent to, and not merely of his knowledge of, the sales. 

Com. v. O'Leary, 143 Mass. 95

An unlawful sale to an agent with notice that he is buying for his principal must be charged as a sale to the principal. A sale to the agent of an undisclosed principal may be alleged either as a sale to the agent or the principal. 

Com. v. Stevens, 155 Mass. 291

A master cannot be held criminally for the acts of his servant if they are done without his knowledge and against his orders. 

Yet if it is an act which is punishable even when done in ignorance, the master and servant are both liable.

CRIMINAL CASES 

47 

Com. v. Galligan, 144 Mass. 171

Defendants tried jointly could be found guilty only by proof that they jointly kept or maintained the nuisance. If one was the sole proprietor and the other only kept or maintained the nuisance as his servant, under his direct supervision, the latter could not be convicted. If in the absence of the employer the servant was authorized to make sales and did so make the sale, both can be convicted. 

Com. v. Gormley, 133 Mass. 580

An allegation of a sale of liquor to T. C. supports a sale to T. F. C. if he was the person named in the complaint as the person to whom the sale was made and was as well known by one name as the other. An allegation of a sale to C is supported by a sale to C, although C in making the purchase was acting as the agent of D; if he did not disclose the fact that he was buying the liquor for D or for some other person. 

Com. v. Keenan, 152 Mass. 9

A son of the defendant told the searching parties that the liquor found was left over from the last raid; he also was seen to give a bottle on the steps of the bar room. Held that there was evidence of the son's agency for the father. 

Com. v. Brady, 147 Mass. 583

A bar keeper was convicted of keeping a nuisance. The defendant worked for another but in the absence of the employer exercised control, and drunken persons were seen coming from the place. 

Com. v. Remby, 2 Gray, 508

When a person buys liquor and informs the seller that he is buying for someone else, the averment must be a sale to that someone else. When a purchase is made by an agent of whose agency the seller has no notice, express or implied, the averment must be a sale to the agent. 

Husband and wife. 

Com. v. Gormley, 133 Mass. 580

The fact that at the time of the sale by a married woman her husband was lying ill in bed in an adjoining room to that where the sale took place, does not raise a conclusive presumption that she was acting under the coercion of her husband.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Carroll, 124 Mass. 30

A married woman sells liquor in a house owned by herself, also the domicile of the family, and as such under the legal control of her husband. If he has knowledge of this and permits it, although he has no interest in the stock of trade or in the profits, he is guilty. 

Com. v. Walsh, 165 Mass. 62

A married woman kept a liquor nuisance. The husband is guilty of having knowledge if he does nothing to prevent her. 

Com. v. Hyland, 155 Mass. 7

A husband and wife kept a tenement. The witness purchased liquor from the wife; he did not see the husband around. The husband testified that he was away when the sales were made, and that if his wife sold any liquor it was against his direction and orders. The fact that the husband and wife lived together in the same tenement is competent evidence that she was his agent. The defendant was convicted as the wife was the agent of the husband. 

Com. v. Coughlin, 80 Mass. 389

Sales made by the wife in the absence of her husband in the husband's house; evidence may be given in support of a complaint against the husband. 

Com. v. Barry, 115 Mass. 146

The wife is not protected by a married woman's certificate; the husband is liable if he had knowledge of her illegal acts and did not prevent them. 

Com. v. Hill, 145 Mass. 305

The husband cannot be convicted where it appears that the wife owned the house and used it for unlawful purposes without his consent. 

Com. v. Tryon, 99 Mass. 442

A proof of an illegal sale by the wife in the husband's absence in an inn kept by both warrants the conviction of both. 

Com. v. Murphy, 68 Mass. 510

A wife may be convicted of selling in the absence of her husband unless it appears that she was acting under his control.

CRIMINAL CASES 

49 

Com. v. Pratt, 126 Mass. 462

A wife hired a hotel, hired the clerk, carried on the business, the liquors seized were her own. The commonwealth showed that at the time the husband assisted his wife. The presumption is that she was acting under his coercion and he can be convicted. 

Letting premises for the sale of liquor. See under Nuisance. 

Com. v. Hayes, 167 Mass. 176

One who lets part of the premises to another with knowledge that liquor was being sold is guilty. (This was not under a lease.) 

Com. v. Burns, 167 Mass. 374

If the defendant had the sole control he is guilty. If he carried it on jointly with another he still would be guilty. 

Com. v. Wentworth, 146 Mass. 36

A lessor cannot be convicted of allowing a tenant to remain after notice of conviction, if the lease reserves specially his control of the building. 

Com. v. Churchill, 136 Mass. 148

The lessor leased the premises to the lessee, who sold liquor illegally. The lessor had knowledge of this when he executed the lease. The lessor cannot be convicted. 

Selling - Pleading. 

Com. v. Mahoney, 134 Mass. 220

At a trial for selling liquor the identity of the offense is not to be determined solely by the date named in the complaint or that given in evidence. 

Com. v. O'Hearn, 132 Mass. 553

The complaint alleged a sale on the day named to one Charles Rock. The complaint was signed Charles J. Rock and the complaint referred to the said Rock. Good complaint. 

Com. v. McNeff, 145 Mass. 406

Sales made before the day named in the complaint may be admitted upon the question as to whether the defendant kept the tenement. 

Com. v. Trainor, 123 Mass. 414

A mistake in the name of the person to whom the sale was made is a material one and a fatal variance. It is a sufficient

CRIMINAL CASES 

statement if the name of the person is the name by which he is generally known at the time of the sale, although he was baptized by a different name. 

Com. v. Stowell, 50 Mass. 569

The pleader need not allege that the liquor was sold in a building. 

Com. v. Finnegan, 124 Mass. 324

If one acting as an agent for another purchases liquor and the complaint alleges a sale to the agent, the defendant must be acquitted. 

Com. v. Burke, 121 Mass. 39 the said "Unlawfully did sell intoxicating liquor to one defendant not having then and there any lawful authority therefor, against the peace," etc., is a good complaint. 

Com. v. O'Leary, 143 Mass. 95

An unlawful sale of liquor to an agent with notice that he is buying for his principal must be alleged as a sale to the principal. A sale to an agent of an undisclosed principal may be alleged as a sale to either the agent or principal. 

Com. v. Thornton, 14 Gray, 41

A complaint which alleges a sale of liquor to a person whose name is to the complainant unknown, is good. 

Com. v. Early, 161 Mass. 186

The complaint alleges a sale to certain persons whose names are unknown, charges several sales to several persons, combines distinct crimes in one count. They cannot for the first time take advantage of this variance on the trial in the Superior 

Court on appeal. 

Com. v. Galligan, 155 Mass. 54

First count, liquor nuisance; second, third and fourth counts, illegally selling. Can be joined. 

Com. v. Purtell, 77 Mass. 78

The pleader need not allege where in the commonwealth the defendant intended to sell the liquor. 

Com. v. Barnard, 72 Mass. 488

The pleader must allege in the Town of and the county of

CRIMINAL CASES 

51 

Com. v. Grey, 68 Mass. 501

A complaint charging the sale of spirituous or intoxicating liquors is bad, even after a plea of nolo contendere. 

Com. v. McGuire, 77 Mass. 460

The complaint may charge a sale to the agent where the principal is not disclosed. 

Com. v. Peckam, 68 Mass. 514

The complaint alleges a sale of intoxicating liquor; a sale of gin is sufficient. 

Com. v. Dillan, 77 Mass. 67 

Com. v. Ryan, 9 Gray, 137

The quantity of liquor sold need not be proved nor the kind sold. 

Com. v. Byrnes, 126 Mass. 248

Under the statute prohibiting the selling except as authorized under this act, the pleader must negative the exception by stating that the defendant had no license, etc. 

Com. v. Wood, 70 Mass. 11 

On the 23d and the 29th the defendant sold one gill of brandy, charges only one sale. 

Com. v. Clark, 80 Mass. 367

The pleader need not allege the kind nor the quantity that it is to be sold in this commonwealth, or with intent to sell it in this commonwealth. 

Tower v. Com., 111 Mass. 417

An allegation: "did sell intoxicating liquors to (name left blank)." This defect is formal, for the fact of selling which is alleged implies that there was a purchaser. 

Com. v. Griffin, 105 Mass. 175

Did sell to a certain person whose name is unknown to the complainant, an allegation that a person not known by the complainant has the significance as to the allegation that it is not known to him. 

Com. v. Burns, 8 Gray, 482 

A sale of liquor on credit is a sale.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Mason v. Lothrop, 7 Gray, 354

One who gives a person a drink in payment for a service is guilty, as this constitutes a sale. 

Com. v. Thornton, 14 Gray, 41

A sale to a person unknown to complainant. If on trial it appears that the name of the person was really known to the complainant at the time of making the complaint, the defendant should be discharged, subject to being tried on a new indictment adapted to the case. 

Com. v. Mahoney, 16 Gray, 20 

Com. v. Dillane, 11 Gray, 67

The allegation of time in the indictment for a single sale is not material and need not be proved as laid. 

Com. v. Shearman, 11 Cush. 546 

An allegation of a sale to George E. Allen is not sustained by proof of a sale to George Allen without evidence that it is the same person. 

Com. v. Hendrie, 2 Gray, 503

A complaint for selling to a person whose name is unknown is supported by evidence of a sale to a person whose name was known to the complainant, if the complainant at the time of making the complaint did not know that the sale was to that person. 

Com. v. Brown, 2 Gray, 358

An indictment was for a sale to Mary Garland. This is not supported by evidence of a sale to one Mary Morrison. At the time of the sale the complainant thought her name was Mary Garland but she had married three months previous to the sale. At the time of the sale her name was Mary Morrison and the complaint was void. 

What constitutes a sale? 

Com. v. O'Hanlon, 155 Mass. 198

Two men drank liquor, one of them paid. It is one sale to the one that paid. 

Com. v. Abrams, 150 Mass. 393

A delivery of liquor on an agreement express or implied that other liquors will be returned in payment for it, is a sale. The

CRIMINAL CASES 

53 statute is to cover every transfer for value, no matter in whatever form the consideration is given. 

Mason v. Lathrop, 73 Mass. 354

A payment of a service is not a sale. 

Com. v. Burns, 74 Mass. 482 

A sale on credit is a sale. 

Com. v. Clark, 80 Mass. 367 

An exchange of grain for liquor is a sale. 

Howard v. Harris, 90 Mass. 297 

A barter is a sale. 

Roberge v. Burnham, 124 Mass. 277

A sale and delivery to a minor by fraud on the part of the minor is a sale to the minor. 

Com. v. Worcester, 126 Mass. 256

The defendant served meals which included liquor, and the fact that the bill included all constitutes a sale. 

Com. v. Reichart, 108 Mass. 482

Evidence that a waiter carried wine into a dining room and brought money back was competent evidence of a sale without more definite proof of payment or promise to pay for the wine; it is a question of fact for the jury. 

Com. v. Rowe, 14 Gray, 47

A delivery of liquor in a shop is prima facie evidence of a sale but not conclusive and can be rebutted. 

Com. v. Clark, 14 Gray, 367

A carried grain to B to distill gin for him. If A received. the liquor distilled from the grain which was carried to B, this would not be a sale, but if he (A) carried grain and received liquor in exchange, this would be a sale. 

Com. v. Packard, 5 Gray, 101

A called for a drink and it was delivered to him by a waiter on the order of the defendant. If it was not paid for, this was not a sale. There must be an agreement to pay in order to make a sale. A sale is a delivery upon compensation made or stipulated to be made.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Knowledge and intent. 

Com. v. Boynton, 2 Allen, 160

One who sells liquor not knowing that it is intoxicating is guilty. The intent is not essential. 

Com. v. Lufkin, 167 Mass. 553

The defendant received some nonintoxicating liquor in bottles; he added some alcohol and then sold it. Guilty of a sale. The finding of dregs in a bottle and men emerging from the premises drunk are facts to be considered by the jury in connection with an illegal sale. 

Com. v. Lanides, 239 Mass. 103

The defendant sold some jamaica ginger. The defendant's intent in making the sale is immaterial. If the article sold is in reality intoxicating liquor, the sale is illegal, even if it is sold as a medicine. The offense is not the intention, it is the act. 

Com. v. Daly, 148 Mass. 428

It is no defense to a complaint for selling liquor that the defendant supposed that the liquor sold by him, which was in fact intoxicating, was a nonintoxicating liquor. 

Com. v. Goodman, 97 Mass. 117

One is guilty even though he did not know that the liquor sold was intoxicating. 

Com. v. O'Kean, 152 Mass. 384

The fact that the defendant did not know that the hop-beer sold by him contained more than the legal per cent and that he honestly believed it to be nonintoxicating is immaterial and will not constitute a defense. 

Com. v. Blos, 116 Mass. 56

Because a liquor is a given per cent of whisky it is not necessarily intoxicating. It is not conclusive, it is a question of fact for the jury as to whether it is or is not intoxicating. 

Com. v. O'Donnell, 143 Mass. 178

Where the defendant introduced evidence that he did not know the percentage of alcohol the commonwealth can introduce evidence that people were seen to go into the house of the defendant sober and come out drunk.

CRIMINAL CASES 

55 

Agent and principal. See under Messenger. 

Com. v. Park, 67 Mass. 553

The selling by John Doe is supported by the selling by his agent or servant. 

Com. v. Woods, 165 Mass. 145

One complained of for selling to a certain person, it is enough to prove a sale to him either as a principal or as agent. 

Com. v. Eddes, 80 Mass. 406

Evidence of sales in the defendant's premises by other persons without other evidence that they were agents of the defendant in the absence of the defendant, except tending to show that the defendant kept the premises, is admissible. 

Com. v. Mrs. Patrick Whalen, 16 Gray, 25

The husband had made a contract to sell liquor; the wife delivered it to the buyer. Delivery is an essential part of a sale. 

If the jury was satisfied that she acted as the agent of her husband in what she knew to be an illegal sale, by making the delivery in his absence, she is guilty. 

Name of the person to whom liquor sold. 

Com. v. Lattinvale, 120 Mass. 385

Where the person selling is licensed, the name of the sellee must be correct; but where the sale is unlicensed it does not matter. 

Com. v. Crawford, 75 Mass. 129

It is no defense to a complaint charging the sale to an unknown person that at the time of making the complaint the knowledge of the officers was from other persons. 

Evidence on the selling of liquor. 

Com. v. Burke, 121 Mass. 39

The complaint charged the sale in the usual form. The defendant filed a motion to quash in that the exceptions should have been charged that the liquors in question were not native wines or cider. Motion denied. 

Com. v. Lattinvale, 120 Mass. 385

In a licensed place a sale to a minor for his parents is a sale to the minor.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Murphy, 155 Mass. 284

A charge of selling without an averment of a delivery is good. The fact that it was procured to be used as evidence will not prohibit a conviction. 

Com. v. Odlin, 40 Mass. 275

In a complaint for selling the pleader need not allege that it was intoxicating nor describe the particular kind of liquor. 

Com. v. Peckham, 2 Gray, 514 

Com. v. Timothy, 8 Gray, 480

An allegation in an indictment of an unlawful sale of intoxicating liquor is supported by proof of such a sale of gin. Need not prove that it was intoxicating. 

Com. v. Sinclair, 138 Mass. 493

In a trial for selling liquor evidence of other sales made by the defendant on the same occasion as that charged in the complaint is admissible. 

Com. v. Stevens, 142 Mass. 457

Witness testified that he purchased liquor from the defendant and produced the same in court. There was no evidence where he had kept it from the time of the sale until he produced the same in court on the date of the trial. After being identified it was admissible in evidence. 

Com. v. Green, 163 Mass. 103 

A waiter in a hotel sold liquor to persons honestly believing that they were bona fide guests of the hotel. Guilty. That one be acting in violation of law is not essential to the offense of selling liquor. 

Com. v. Galligan, 156 Mass. 270

A sale of liquor after the date charged in the complaint is admissible. 

Com. v. Leach, 246 Mass. 464

Marked money which the defendant received for the sale of the liquor, and which later was found on the person of the defendant, constitutes a sale and is not a subterfuge. 

Com. v. Coolidge, 138 Mass. 193

A single sale may be made, although made in the ordinary course of business.

CRIMINAL CASES 

57 

Com. v. Blood, 4 Gray, 31

A complaint for selling liquor to unknown persons on August 15, 1854, on which the defendant was convicted in the lower court is not supported on trial in the Superior Court by proof of a sale to different persons in 1853. 

Com. v. Shea, 160 Mass. 160

Liquor was found on October 2 in a house from which a traveled path led to the saloon of the defendant. Evidence of a sale made on August 25 not too remote. On two occasions drunken men were seen coming out. The defendant was seen going back and forth between the two places. Guilty. 

Com. v. Wallace, 123 Mass. 400

Liquors were stored in one house, although the liquors were sold in another. Guilty. 

Com. v. Ryan, 75 Mass. 137 

A complaint for selling liquors without any authority, etc., the burden of proof is on the defendant to show the same. 

Com. v. Tinkham, 80 Mass. 12

An unlawful sale can be proved by other witnesses than the purchaser. 

Com. v. McCaughey, 75 Mass. 296

A complaint for keeping a certain building for the illegal sale of liquor is not maintained by proof of the occupying of one of the several tenements in the building. 

Com. v. Hogan, 97 Mass. 120

The defendant, with three men, played cards for the drinks; the three all lost and treated accordingly. This is evidence of three distinct sales.

Exposing and keeping liquors for sale - Pleading. 

Com. v. Doyle, 132 Mass. 244

In a complaint for exposing and keeping the allegation of time is immaterial and need not be proved as laid, it being enough to prove that the offense was committed any time during the statute of limitations. The complaint charged a day certain. 

Com. v. Davis, 121 Mass. 352

In a complaint for exposing and keeping the exceptions in the following section need not be negatived in that the allegation of

CRIMINAL CASES 

selling, etc., without having any licensed authority or appointment covers this. 

Com. v. Sprague, 128 Mass. 75

A complaint alleging the defendant did keep intoxicating liquors with intent to sell the same in this commonwealth, he not being authorized to sell the same, sets forth an offense. 

Com. v. Finnerty, 148 Mass. 162

The illegal exposing and keeping is a continuing offense which may extend over a long or short period of time; evidence can be admitted three weeks after the making of a complaint. 

Com. v. Chisholm, 103 Mass. 213 

Com. v. Snow, 80 Mass. 20 

Exposing and keeping may be pleaded continuando. 

Com. v. Wright, 12 Allen, 190 "Knowingly and willfully did keep a tenement for the illegal sale and illegal keeping for sale" is a good allegation. 

Com. v. Walton, 11 Allen, 238

When pleaded continuando, evidence may be introduced on the first day, the last day, and during the period. 

Com. v. Vincent, 165 Mass. 18

Exposing and keeping is in its nature a continuing one, and evidence is admissible tending to show conditions before and after the date named, within reasonable limits. 

Evidence of dates other than pleaded in the complaint. 

Com. v. Carr, 111 Mass. 423 

The complaint charged a day certain, June 29. Several days prior the defendant sold liquors. Time is not essential and is admissible. 

Com. v. Dearbon, 109 Mass. 368

An admission made that five months prior to the date of the complaint, July 1871, he was the owner of a shop has a tendency to show that he is still the owner. He was also seen in that shop during that time. Evidence of sales made prior to the date is competent as tending to show what use the defendant made of the shop.

CRIMINAL CASES 

59 

Com. v. Maloney, 112 Mass. 283

Defendant was charged with exposing and keeping on September 10. On the appeal the evidence was confined to that day. It is true that in criminal prosecutions the exact date need not necessarily be proved as laid but every complaint must allege a precise date. In the above case there was one punishment, but subsequently the law was changed; hence the date in the complaint in this class of cases must be proved as laid. 

Com. v. Berry, 109 Mass. 366

Complaint alleged January 12. The next week liquor was found in the place and in the interim men were seen leaving the place. Admissible. 

Com. v. Stohr, 109 Mass. 365

Defendant was charged with exposing and keeping liquors on January 22. Evidence could be introduced of liquor seized in the same place three weeks before the date and of sales made there after the seizure and tending to show that the liquors found on the premises on the day named were kept by the defendant with intent to sell. 

Com. v. Doyle, 132 Mass. 244

In a complaint for exposing and keeping the allegation of time is immaterial and need not be proved as laid, it being enough to prove that the offense was committed within the statute of limitations; the complaint charged a day certain. 

Com. v. Hurley, 158 Mass. 159 

Com. v. Dillane, 1 Gray, 483

Complaint alleged on a day certain. The commonwealth can introduce evidence of violations 30 days previous. 

Com. v. Neylon, 159 Mass. 541 

Complaint charged violations from July 18 to August 19.

Evidence is admissible on August 30 and October 19 as being not too remote. 

Com. v. Matthews, 129 Mass. 485

Upon the question of intent the acts of the defendant some time after as well as shortly before are admissible. The keeping might be inferred and the commonwealth might prove a continuous keeping in ten days after the date of the complaint.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Gagne, 153 Mass. 205

Two counts, one charging January 23 and one charging January 25. The commonwealth can introduce evidence of violations seven months previous. Not too remote. 

Com. v. Patalone, 254 Mass. 69 

A raid was made four days after the date in the complaint.

Implements were seized and the jury may well have inferred from their nature that they were on the premises before the last date in the complaint. 

Com. v. Carney, 152 Mass. 566 

The complaint avers from the first day of May to the 18th of

May the defendant exposed and kept liquors. Evidence on the 18th admissible. 

Com. v. Vincent, 165 Mass. 18

Evidence, within 8 weeks of the day on which the offense is charged is admissible to show the intent on the day charged. 

Evidence after the day is also admissible. 

Com. v. Kyne, 162 Mass. 146

A statement made to a United States marshal by the defendant 4 months after regarding the liquor on the premises is admissible. 

Com. v. Henderson, 140 Mass. 303 

Evidence 2 days before the date in the complaint is admissible. 

Com. v. Finnerty, 148 Mass. 162

Evidence 3 weeks before the date of the complaint is admissible as the offense is a continuing one. 

Com. v. Powers, 123 Mass. 244

Evidence of the condition of a room at 8 o'clock A.M. of the day after the date of the complaint as to appointments and fixtures is admissible if connected with other evidence connecting the defendant with the crime charged.

Exposing and keeping - Evidence. No traffic. 

Com. v. De Finnis, 263 Mass. 138 

No illegal sales. No traffic. On a search the officers found 210 bottles of beer and some wine. In the cellar they found tables, a copper boiler, and some settees. Guilty.

CRIMINAL CASES 

61 

Com. v. McManus, 161 Mass. 64

Liquors and implements were found in the house. No evidence of a sale. Guilty. 

Com. v. Cummings, 121 Mass. 63

No traffic, no sales. Officers found in the defendant's house a bar and several men sitting around a table drinking. The defendant formerly had a license and was waiting for another one. 

The court charged if the host (the defendant) did not sell, it was up to him to call them as witnesses; and his not doing so, the court could take that fact into consideration. 

Com. v. Brothers, 158 Mass. 200

Found two men in a shop, gas turned down, glasses on a shelf smelling of liquor, a demijohn near by, cider in the cellar recently opened, bottles of ale and other liquor in barrels in the cellar. Guilty. 

Com. v. Canney, 158 Mass. 210

Finding large quantities of liquor in the store of the defendant. 

The jury might be satisfied beyond a reasonable doubt that it was kept for illegal sales. There is no presumption that it was illegally kept for sale. No evidence of sales and no men seen coming out drunk. Liquor was found in the safe, which was opened by the proprietor. Guilty. 

Com. v. Leo, 110 Mass. 414 

Officers found beer mugs and dripping pans and tumblers, the defendant behind the bar, and two men drinking beer. A keg on tap. No sales and no other kind of liquor found. The burden of proof is on the commonwealth to show whether it was lager or strong beer. 

Com. v. Van Stone, 97 Mass. 548 

Men were seen coming out drunk; resorted to by both men and women, most of them drunk. On search, found liquor and accessories. No sales. Enough to convict. 

Com. v. Higgins, 16 Gray, 19 

Room contained a bar, show case and oyster bar, a toddy stick, and barrel of ale. On one occasion saw a woman drunk and occasionally saw persons going away drunk. Guilty. 

Com. v. Hoffman, 258 Mass. 410

The place was a dry goods store, frequented by well-known drinkers. The officers found the usual accompaniments of illegal

CRIMINAL CASES 

sales of liquor and found bottles wrapped up in the cash drawer which the defendant tried to conceal. Guilty. 

Com. v. Tenney, 148 Mass. 452

Liquor was found in the barn a short distance from the house owned by the defendant. The liquor in the barn was tagged with the name of the defendant where the defendant was found. Guilty. 

Com. v. Martin, 162 Mass. 402

A finding of large quantities of liquor in a shop justifies a verdict of guilty. 

Com. v. Wallace, 123 Mass. 400

The defendant occupied the front room as a grocery, the back room was a kitchen. In the kitchen liquor was found in a flask. In the closet was found a funnel and glasses. In the cellar they found a bottle of whisky buried. No sales nor traffic. Guilty. 

Com. v. Gafley, 122 Mass. 334

Officers found a quantity of ale and whisky on the premises, the dwelling house of the defendant. On one occasion saw people go in and out. A carriage stopped and a person emerged with a pitcher and glasses and said in response to a question that it was better than water. Guilty. 

Com. v. Atkins, 136 Mass. 160

Officers found 120 bottles of ale in a barrel all headed up. No traffic. No exposing for sale. Exposing and keeping is but one offense and is supported by proof of either. It is a question of fact for the jury whether the defendant kept the liquors for sale. 

Com. v. Tate, 178 Mass. 121

Presence of a large stock of liquor, together with the presence of people drinking in a room, is sufficient to warrant a conviction for exposing and keeping. 

Com. v. Levy, 126 Mass. 240

In the dwelling house of the defendant the officers found in the closet of the dining room a barrel of ale, some other liquor, tumblers, strainer, a bowl, and some dishes. In the shed an empty barrel was found. The defendant was found with a boarder with a glass of ale in his hand. No sales nor traffic. Evidence that the furniture (tumblers, etc.) was appropriate to a place of public

CRIMINAL CASES 

63 resort and furnished an indication of an intent to sell rather than to apply to a mere domestic use of a single family. 

Com. v. Wallace, 143 Mass. 88

Frequent presence of drunken persons on the street and street corners, if near the premises of the defendant, might be evidence that those persons obtained liquor on the premises of the defendant. 

Com. v. Kelley, 152 Mass. 486

No liquor found in the tenement of the defendant. In the shed in the rear and adjacent were found two barrels of ale, one open, one closed, with a pitcher under the closed one. Persons had been seen going in the direction of the shed as if for a drink, accompanied by the defendant; also persons seen carrying away pails. Guilty.

Possession enough to convict. 

Com. v. Fraher, 126 Mass. 56

Although the liquor cannot be seen, the defendant can be convicted of exposing and keeping. 

Com. v. Hanley, 140 Mass. 457

A complaint for exposing and keeping is not the same offense as a liquor nuisance, although the evidence is the same in both cases. 

Com. v. Dolan, 121 Mass. 374

Whether the defendant exposes or keeps for sale or both exposes and keeps it is but offense, and a complaint charging both is good and is supported by proof of either. 

Com. v. McCue, 121 Mass. 358

A man may have liquor and intend to sell it, but unless he exposes it for sale he does not commit any crime under the complaint. 

Com. v. Welch, 140 Mass. 372

A complaint for exposing and keeping with intent to sell is supported by proof that the defendant kept such liquors although he did not expose them for sale. 

Com. v. Meskil, 165 Mass. 142

One may be convicted of illegally keeping liquor for sale without proof that he actually sold any or offered or exposed them for sale.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Ahearn, 228 Mass. 547

The defendant can be convicted of proof of keeping with intent to sell unlawfully without any evidence of exposing liquors for sale; may be established without evidence of a sale or attempt to sell. 

Com. v. Tay, 146 Mass. 146

A complaint for exposing and keeping is supported by proof for such keeping for sale merely, and the averment of exposure may be rejected as surplusage.

Liquors kept in one place and sold in another. 

Com. v. Cronin, 117 Mass. 140

The defendant was complained of for exposing and keeping liquors in A. The defendant boarded in the house of B, five rods distant. Evidence of sales in A. Evidence can be introduced showing that liquor was carried from B to A, and that liquor was sold in the house of B. 

Com. v. Fomuck, 261 Mass. 292

Officer testified that he had seen men entering the defendant's house, that he knew them to be drinking men, talked to them when they came out and smelled liquor on their breath. The defendant had a store next to his which was vacant, and to which the defendant had a key. Liquor was found in the vacant store. Guilty. 

Com. v. McCullow, 140 Mass. 370

The defendant had a place where liquor was seized. A passageway led to a house in which his mother lived, and liquor was found there on a search. The defendant had access to the latter place. The evidence of the liquor found in his mother's house could be introduced against the defendant. 

Com. v. McKenna, 158 Mass. 207

The defendant kept a store. An unoccupied store was next door and both were connected by a back entry common to both doors. Liquor was found in the unoccupied store. No evidence that the defendant has access to the unoccupied store. In the defendant's store was one quart of whisky, the defendant being absent in the cellar of the defendant's store to which other tenants had access. The defendant lived with his grandmother 

150 yards away. Liquor was found in her house. The defendant was seen to pass from her house to his store but carried no

CRIMINAL CASES 

65 bundles. Men congregated outside of the store drunk; one half of a pint of beer and eight empties were found in the store, also whisky glasses with an odor. Men were found in the back room of the defendant's store. Guilty. 

Com. v. Kern, 147 Mass. 595

The crime of exposing and keeping is not local in its nature and it is sufficient if the proof shows that the crime was committed in the county. 

Com. v. Murphy, 153 Mass. 290

Liquor was found in the defendant's place, he was seen to go across the street and return to his own place. Liquor was found in the latter. Evidence that it was a storehouse. Guilty. 

Com. v. Kowalsky, 243 Mass. 538

If the whisky found in the house of the defendant was kept with intent to sell it contrary to law, it would be immaterial that it was not intended to be sold in the place where it was found. 

If kept in the defendant's house with the intent by him to sell it at his place of business, he is guilty of the offense charged. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 116 Mass. 24

Liquors were kept in a dwelling house and intended for sale. 

It is not necessary to prove that the sales were to be made in the dwelling house; it is sufficient to show that this was a magazine or warehouse where the liquors were stored to be sold in some other place.

Circumstances surrounding the place - Atmosphere and implements, etc. 

Com. v. Wallace, 123 Mass. 400

The defendant kept a place consisting of two rooms, in one of them a counter and a few cigars, tables and chairs, and the place is one of common resort. On being arrested the defendant tried to conceal some bottles. A few days after his arrest he tried to bribe the officer. Flight, concealment and the use of falsehoods are evasions and are matters which the court or jury can consider. Guilty. 

Com. v. Hurley, 158 Mass. 159

The complaint was pleaded on a day certain. The commonwealth can introduce evidence of violations 30 days previous.

Drunken men were seen going in and coming out. The defendant

CRIMINAL CASES 

introduced evidence that these men got their liquor from other premises, and this is a question of fact for the jury. When the defendant got to the station house he threw away a bottle containing whisky. This is competent evidence as being a part of the res gestae. 

Com. v. Gay, 153 Mass. 211

The commonwealth can introduce evidence of persons going into a place sober and coming out drunk, and some others very drunk. 

Three quarts found on the search. One quart found on the purchase from the defendant. Numerous persons going in and coming out of the premises of the defendant under circumstances indicating that liquor was sold. Guilty. 

Com. v. Zardon, 253 Mass. 601

On search one pint of liquor found. Bottles, one with an odor; large quantities of stoppers. On six or seven occasions during a period of two months there was travel to and from the store by persons under the influence of liquor. Guilty. 

Com. v. Aaron, 114 Mass. 255

Evidence is admissible that witnesses have seen liquor delivered in response to calls for the same. 

Com. v. Powderly, 148 Mass. 357

The officer testified that he saw men at the bar drinking, it being fitted up with glasses and liquor of all kinds. He saw liquor of the color of whisky but could not swear that it was liquor. Guilty. 

Com. v. Locke, 145 Mass. 401

Officer testified that in the absence of the defendant on the raid the barkeeper tried to conceal the liquor. The jury might infer that the liquor was kept for unlawful purpose from its presence and the attempt to conceal it. 

Com. v. Matthews, 129 Mass. 485

When the business is continuous it is allowable to show the general course of business, such as fittings, furnishings, the keeping of the goods or liquor apparently for sale at about the time charged in the complaint.

CRIMINAL CASES 

67 

Com. v. Mead, 140 Mass. 300

Persons frequenting the house, not living there; persons under the influence of liquor coming out; drunken men found present; signs of recent drinking. Guilty. 

Com. v. Hoar, 121 Mass. 375

A single sale of liquor in a public place tends to prove exposing and keeping of such liquor for sale. The defendant, a bar tender, sold three men whisky, and that is all the liquor that was in view. Guilty. 

Com. v. Peto, 136 Mass. 155

The witness met the defendant one-half mile from the house. 

He asked the defendant, "Is it there?" Defendant replied yes.

The witness then went to the house of the defendant and handed the wife of the defendant a bottle. He and she had no conversation, and he paid her 25 cents. Liquor smelled alcoholic. The witness drank from the bottle and went to sleep. Guilty. 

Com. v. Dailey, 133 Mass. 577

The officer testified that he entered the room on a search warrant, that the lights were put out, and that afterwards he saw the defendant break a bottle in the room and found there a number of broken bottles; that there was an odor of beer in the room. 

If the defendant purposely broke a bottle of beer, he acknowledged he kept it for sale, to keep it away from the officers. His act might be considered as bearing on the question whether the defendant thought the beer was intoxicating. 

Com. v. Riley, 157 Mass. 89

The officers entered the premises, found servants of the defendant and others not in the employ drinking; the defendant was a mile away. The servants testified that no sales were made on Sunday and the defendant had frequently instructed them not to sell on Sunday. Guilty. 

Com. v. Ham, 150 Mass. 122

Employees of the place seen running away with jugs from the officer. Employee delivering jugs from teams bearing the name of the defendant. Teams bearing his name delivering jugs. Guilty. 

Com. v. Lynch, 164 Mass. 541

The conduct of the defendant when his house was searched by a warrant, warrants an inference being drawn that the liquors found secreted were kept for an unlawful purpose.

68 

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Moore, 157 Mass. 324

Officers found a woman beside a jug ready to pay for the same, liquor being seen going into the house, jugs being thrown out of the window, whisky on the side of the barn. Guilty. 

Com. v. Berry, 109 Mass. 366

On the day that liquor was found the court must find that on that day the defendant kept the liquor with intent to sell. 

Com. v. Henderson, 140 Mass. 303

Evidence of sale two days prior to date is admissible. 

Com. v. Cleary, 105 Mass. 384

Officers found a small portion of liquor in the house of the defendant under circumstances tending to show that it was for sale. Possession is sufficient if it be on a single occasion and for a short time. 

Com. v. Davenport, 2 Allen, 299

Evidence is admissible that the defendant told various persons that he intended to sell; casks and barrels were seen in his yard, and men were seen unloading them there. Guilty. 

Com. v. Curran, 119 Mass. 206

The burden of proof is on the commonwealth to prove that the liquors exposed and kept for sale were intoxicating, and the jury must acquit if the evidence left them in doubt as to the fact. 

Com. v. O'Reilly, 116 Mass. 15

If the defendant was in possession of the liquor and exercising control, he is guilty even if he was not the owner. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 116 Mass. 24

Under the statute the mere possession of liquors is not an offense. 

Com. v. Hayes, 114 Mass. 282

Intent to sell unlawfully may and generally must be proved by circumstantial evidence; it is a question of fact for the jury. 

Com. v. Goodman, 97 Mass. 117

The statute prohibits absolutely the keeping of liquors with intent to sell. The intent applies solely to the purpose for which they are kept, and not at all to the nature or quality of the article. Thus, a person is bound to know or ascertain at his peril.

CRIMINAL CASES 

69 

Com. v. Carolin, 2 Allen, 169

There is a distinction between merely keeping, and keeping and using the tenement for the illegal purpose. 

Liquors found in the pocket. 

Com. v. Kern, 147 Mass. 595

Liquors may be kept on the person of the defendant, which is a violation. 

Com. v. McDonald, 147 Mass. 527

A and B were together on the fair grounds. B had a bottle of liquor and some glasses in his pocket, which he passed to others to drink, he accepting the money in payment. A was arrested and a bottle was found on his person. B testified that the crowd all pooled together to buy the liquor one mile away from the grounds. Enough to convict A. The evidence of A, who was apart from B when B was arrested was admissible. They were acting as accomplices in the keeping of liquor with intent to sell. 

Com. v. Donnelly, 246 Mass. 507 

Com. v. Wilkins, 243 Mass. 356

Liquor was seized from the person of the defendant and is admissible in evidence against him, no matter whether the seizure was legal or not. 

Com. v. Ryan, 160 Mass. 172

Liquor was found in a pocket. The fact that it was in the pocket does not make any difference. It is keeping. 

Liquors in a vehicle. See under Illegal Transportation. 

Com. v. Kern, 147 Mass. 595

Liquor may be kept for illegal sale in a movable vehicle. 

Com. v. Munchnikoff, 257 Mass. 181

The defendant was the keeper of a garage. A driver testified that he drove an automobile into the garage of the defendant where the loads of liquor were transferred. He saw the defendant there at the time. At another garage owned by the defendant liquor was seized in a truck. There were not any records kept in the garage of automobiles entering or leaving. Guilty of conspiracy.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Ryan, 160 Mass. 172

A person can be convicted of an offense from a moving vehicle. The offense is not local in its nature. 

Com. v. McConnell, 77 Mass. 204

Liquors kept and peddled from house to house in a wagon can be complained of for exposing and keeping for sale. It makes no difference whether the liquors are kept in a building or a wagon and whether there was a horse harnessed to the wagon or not. 

Knowledge of the alcoholic contents. 

Com. v. Brelsford, 161 Mass. 61

It is not whether the liquors were actually intoxicating but whether they were over the illegal per cent. 

Com. v. Lacourse, 257 Mass. 229

On a day certain liquor was purchased at the home of the defendant; liquor was seized on a subsequent day. The complaint need not show that it was intoxicating. The mere keeping of the liquor is not a crime; it is only when it is kept unlawfully. 

Com. v. Savery, 145 Mass. 212

It is no defense that the liquors were on the premises of the defendant by mistake, he did not know that they were intoxicating, if he intended to sell them and they were in fact intoxicating. 

Query: If the liquor was there and could not be told from nonintoxicating liquor, and the defendant intended to have an analysis made, could he be convicted? 

Com. v. Campopiano, 254 Mass. 560

Testimony that the purchaser of the liquor drank some and became intoxicated is properly admitted, not to prove the alcoholic contents but to be weighed with other evidence. The effect of the liquor may be so great that a jury could infer that it contained more than the legal per cent of alcohol. 

Com. v. Batchelder, 109 Mass. 361

It is not necessary to prove or allege that the liquor was spirituous or distilled.

Other evidence of exposing and keeping continued. law. 

Com. v. McDonough, 95 Mass. 581

Exposing and keeping is not pro se a nuisance at common

CRIMINAL CASES 

71 

Com. v. Welch, 142 Mass. 473

Officer who searched the premises testified that a tumbler that he seized contained intoxicating liquor. He did not produce the tumbler or account for its absence. Does not come within the meaning of secondary evidence. 

Com. v. Kazules, 246 Mass. 564

The commonwealth cannot introduce evidence that frequenters of a place were of a nationality and presumably of a language not of the defendant's own. It can introduce evidence that people entered in such numbers at such time and in such a manner as when considered with other circumstances to warrant a conclusion that liquors were sold even if there is no evidence of intoxication or of bottles, etc. 

Com. v. Godley, 77 Mass. 454

A complaint for keeping a tenement is supported by proof that he used only a part. 

Com. v. Welch, 84 Mass. 510

A complaint that the defendant kept a certain tenement is supported by evidence that he used the cellar. 

Com. v. Maloney, 113 Mass. 211

The fact that the defendant was seen in a room adjoining the bar in which the liquors were kept is not enough to convict. He was not called upon to explain his presence there. 

Com. v. Kimball, 105 Mass. 465

To keep may in its ordinary and more obvious sense apply only to one who exercises control or proprietorship of the building or the place used. To maintain has no such limited application. The building is not maintained by the occupant, but the nuisance is maintained by prosecuting the illegal traffic. 

Com. v. Sheehan, 143 Mass. 468 

An averment from August 1 to September 3 charges but one crime. Evidence on September 3 may be introduced. 

Common nuisance - -Pleading. 

Com. v. Quinlan, 153 Mass. 482

In a complaint for a nuisance the pleader need not allege that the place so used; a tenement is sufficient.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Adams, 67 Mass. 481

A complaint which alleges from day to the day of the indictment, finding and presentment is defective and fatal. 

Com. v. Welsch, 83 Mass. 1

The time alleged covered both before and after the enactment of the General statutes is good. 

Com. v. Davenport, 84 Mass. 299

The short form of a liquor nuisance is good. 

Com. v. Hoye, 75 Mass. 292

From that day to the day of making this complaint charges a nuisance during the whole of the time. 

Com. v. Clark, 145 Mass. 251

Liquor nuisances, ill fame, gaming, all charged in the one complaint and extending over a period of 16 months, held sufficient. 

Com. v. Sampson, 113 Mass. 191

The pleader need not allege by whom the tenement was kept or used. 

Com. v. Murphy, 11 Gray, 454

A building may be a tenement, as the defendant occupied a shop consisting of one room in a building affixed to the soil, it being the only room in the building. The pleader can allege it as a tenement. 

Com. v. Ryan, 136 Mass. 436

Form for a. The defendant used the tenement for the illegal exposing and keeping is sufficient. No act of the defendant was to the injury of the citizens of the commonwealth. The complaint need not charge illegal sale or illegal keeping. 

Single sale. 

Com. v. Hayes, 150 Mass. 506 

A single sale of liquor is not sufficient to convict on a complaint for keeping a nuisance for the illegal keeping and sale of liquor. 

Com. v. Coolidge, 138 Mass. 193

The complaint charged a nuisance. Two persons in a drug store were asked what soda they wanted and they replied whisky.

CRIMINAL CASES 

73 Only one sale. Although the complaint covered a period of time, the court could take into consideration the manner in which the sales were made and attending circumstances; and was sufficient to convict. 

Com. v. McNeff, 145 Mass. 406

The sales on two different occasions will support a complaint for a liquor nuisance.

Com. v. Carney, 152 Mass. 566 

Complaint charges from May 1 to May 18; evidence of violations on the 18th admissible. 

Com. v. Briggs, 52 Mass. 573

On a day certain and divers other times since. No evidence of violations before is admissible. 

Com. v. Kelley, 116 Mass. 341 

The complaint charges a nuisance from June 1 to October 19.

The witness was allowed to testify that he saw the defendant at the same place April 4 and purchased liquor from the defendant there. Admissible. 

Com. v. Dunn, 111 Mass. 426 

On October 28 and divers other days, etc. Evidence was admitted on October 28 or for the whole period. The indictment is for an offense during a single period. Not bad for duplicity. 

Com. v. Wood, 70 Mass. 13

A complaint for a common seller from a day certain to divers times since; any violation during that time is admissible. 

Evidence - Nuisance. 

Com. v. Hanley, 140 Mass. 457

A complaint for exposing and keeping is not the same offense as a nuisance, although the same evidence is used in both cases. 

Com. v. Hughes, 154 Mass. 598 

Empty bottles loaded from a wagon, full bottles loaded thereon. The team was followed and the driver was seen to make a delivery to a house. Officers visiting the defendant's house had there seen similar bottles. Guilty.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Lyons, 160 Mass. 174

Two buildings were connected. The defendant carried liquor from one house to another, men were seen going in. The defendant seen making frequent trips. On a search, liquor was found. Guilty. 

Com. v. Patterson, 153 Mass. 5

A lot of land with detached buildings devisable into several tenements, but not so divided, if kept and used for liquor selling constitutes a single nuisance. A single nuisance may be pleaded as one tenement. 

Com. v. Line, 149 Mass. 65

A plea of guilty of selling liquor is admissible on a complaint for maintaining a nuisance, if followed by evidence that it is the same place. 

Carlton v. Rugg, 149 Mass. 550

The equity court has jurisdiction to abate a nuisance for liquor, gaming, or prostitution and is constitutional. 

Com. v. Bacon, 108 Mass. 26 

Com. v. Hersey, 144 Mass. 297

The offense of a nuisance is local in its nature and the proof and the averment must correspond. The complaint alleged that 

Bacon of Middlebury kept and sold liquor in Middlebury. The evidence showed that he kept in Worcester. It is a fatal variance. 

Com. v. Kane, 150 Mass. 294

Liquor found concealed. Utensils smelled of liquor, men found in the alley back of place drunk men drinking from bottles. Guilty. 

Com. v. Chaney, 148 Mass. 6

Evidence may be introduced that liquor not purchased from the defendant was consumed on the premises of the defendant. 

Com. v. McCabe, 163 Mass. 98

Officer can testify that he saw men present at the house of the defendant, the presence of bottle of beer, that witness asked for, received and paid for beer. 

Com. v. Dowdican, 114 Mass. 257

The witness was asked what the defendant had been doing for the past six months; his answer was "selling whisky." Admissible.

CRIMINAL CASES 

75 

Com. v. Campbell, 116 Mass. 32

The commonwealth need not show and prove delivery of the liquor, but a sale may be shown by circumstantial evidence, the manner in which the place is kept, the furniture and the liquors found there, the number of persons found about the premises, these are all circumstances tending to show the illegal sale of liquor. 

Com. v. Glennan, 116 Mass. 46

Evidence that it was a regular drinking place, that the defendant kept it, that ale was poured down the sink, the defendant denying that it was ale, a bar in the place and men in front of it, tumblers on the bar. Guilty. 

Com. v. Welch, 2 Allen, 510

A cellar is part of the building, so an indictment charging that the defendant kept and maintained a tenement for the illegal sale, etc., is a good complaint. 

Com. v. Callahan, 108 Mass. 421

During the time covered by the complaint the defendant admitted a sale, and said to the officer that he would plead guilty if he could get off with a fine. Admissible. 

Com. v. Callahan, 108 Mass. 421

In the basement was a beer pump, kept as a saloon, a counter therein, tumblers smelling of whisky; officers seized brandy, and whisky was found in the second story where the defendant lived. 

The defendant admitted that he sold whisky. No sales. Guilty. 

Com. v. McShane, 110 Mass. 502

During the time covered by the complaint an officer saw the defendant in the building twice and made a complaint for exposing and keeping liquors for sale: No sales. The defendant can be convicted of keeping a nuisance. Two different offenses. 

Com. v. Owens, 114 Mass. 252

On complaint for a nuisance the commonwealth need not prove that the defendant kept it all of the time alleged in the complaint or that the premises were in other respects disorderly or a nuisance. 

Com. v. Pierce, 107 Mass. 487

The indictment was for keeping a place and tenement for the illegal keeping of liquor. The defendant had a saloon containing

CRIMINAL CASES 

a bar and liquors. He slept overhead. Liquors were found in the cellar of the building. There was no evidence connecting them with the saloon. Guilty. 

Com. v. Kingsley, 108 Mass. 24

The defendant kept a saloon downstairs where he had a license to sell ale. Upstairs and connected by an elevator the commonwealth found strong liquor. The defendant said he could not make a living unless he sold liquor. No sales. Guilty. 

Com. v. Heywood, 105 Mass. 187

There was evidence that the defendant sold home brew. 

Persons were seen coming from the house with jugs, pitchers and pails but could not connect them with the defendant, or that they contained anything. Guilty. 

Com. v. Graves, 97 Mass. 114

Indictment for being a common seller; the only evidence that on a single day three distinct sales were made. Guilty. 

Com. v. Cutter, 97 Mass. 336 

There was a bar, tumblers on the counter, a beer pump, a few cigars. Men present drunk. Persons there tried to hide a jug and attempted to pass out a bottle when the officers entered. Enough to constitute a sale as a common seller, which today is a nuisance. 

Com. v. Wallace, 123 Mass. 401

Liquors stored in one house complained of as a nuisance. 

This is good although the liquors were sold in another. 

Com. v. Odlin, 40 Mass. 275

Complained of being a common seller. Different sales to the same person. The defendant is liable. 

Com. v. Hayes, 150 Mass. 506

A single sale is not sufficient to convict on a complaint for keeping a nuisance for the exposing and keeping of liquor. 

Com. v. Dobbyn, 14 Gray, 45 

Witnesses called for Medford and strong beer; they were given small beer which was not intoxicating. Guilty. 

Com. v. Cogan, 107 Mass. 212 

Com. v. Gallagher, 1 Allen, 592

A tenement for the illegal keeping for sale of liquor may be in a shop consisting of one room, but not forming a part of the

CRIMINAL CASES 

77 dwelling house. A complaint for exposing and keeping and maintaining a nuisance from April 6 to October 5 may be convicted for a violation of the statute on a single occasion. 

Com. v. Deady, 7 Allen, 531

Not necessary to convict that the defendant made three distinct sales by positive evidence, but if on circumstantial evidence the jury finds that the defendant made as many as three sales, he is guilty. 

Com. v. Hill, 4 Allen, 589

The burden of proof is on the commonwealth beyond a reasonable doubt that the tenement described in the complaint was used by the defendant for the illegal keeping of liquor. 

Com. v. Welch, 1 Allen, 1

The evidence must be sufficient to establish the illegal keeping for sale, and not a mere place for the purpose of making sales. 

Com. v. McArty, 11 Gray, 456

An indictment for the keeping of a tenement, etc., is sustained by the proof of keeping a shop. Two or a large number of sales would not constitute a nuisance, for the jury must find a constantly keeping and making a business of selling. 

Com. v. Taylor, 15 Gray, 26

Witnesses testified that beer was sold and that it was not intoxicating. Persons were seen going in and coming out drunk. 

Persons hustled cents to see who would pay for the drinks. Guilty of liquor nuisance by gaming. Indicted for liquor house of ill fame and gaming. 

Com. v. McCauhey, 9 Gray, 296

An indictment which charges the defendant with keeping a building used for liquor selling is not supported by proof that the building occupied by the defendant was only a part. 

Com. v. Patterson, 138 Mass. 498

Complaint for a liquor nuisance, two sales made. A building cannot be said to be used for the illegal sales of liquor which makes it a nuisance nor can the proprietor be said to keep and maintain on the strength of a single sale made without premeditation. These words import a certain degree of permanence.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Sherman v. Wilder, 106 Mass. 537

A landlord cannot receive rent from premises if he has knowledge that the same were used illegally and parol evidence is admissible to prove the same. 

Com. v. Kerrissey, 141 Mass. 110

A complaint charged a liquor nuisance for the sale and keeping of liquor is supported by proof of such illegal keeping on a single occasion within the time covered by the complaint, and it is unnecessary to prove that the defendant used the premises for the illegal purpose on other days and times. On a search one day the police found a bottle of rum. 

Com. v. Slosson, 152 Mass. 489

The fact that several people were seen coming out of a tenement, that is evidence to be considered upon the question whether intoxicating liquors were sold there. 

The mere fact that a large number of people were seen going into a drug store on a day certain furnished evidence proper to be considered by the jury in connection with other circumstances of a significant character as indicating liquor was sold there. 

Club violations. 

Com. v. Pomphret, 137 Mass. 564

Members pay an admission fee on joining. No outsiders own any liquors. The club employs a steward who dispenses the liquor to members who pay for it with checks representing sums, which checks were purchased from the steward. The money received from the sales is used for the purpose of purchasing liquors. Not guilty of exposing and keeping. 

Com. v. Wolz, 219 Mass. 37

A steward who sold liquors to members of an incorporated club having no license is guilty. No defense that he is an employee. 

Com. v. Ryan, 152 Mass. 283

Persons had liquor in their locker. Fixtures resembling a bar room, many men found drinking out of a bottle and not bearing the right names. Merely a subterfuge and guilty of exposing and keeping. 

Com. v. Smith, 102 Mass. 144

The members of a club jointly contributed money to purchase of liquors by the steward. The steward issued checks to the mem-

CRIMINAL CASES 

79 bers who purchased the liquor. If the jury finds that the liquor belonged to the members, that the steward merely kept the liquor, he was not guilty; if the jury finds this to be an evasion, then he is guilty. Druggist. 

Com. v. Brothers, 158 Mass. 200

A druggist is not only liable to punishment for the offense but to a forfeiture of his license, and this is not double jeopardy. 

Com. v. Banks, 156 Mass. 233

A conviction of the defendant does not automatically void the license. 

Com. v. Pierce, 147 Mass. 160

A druggist selling alcohol without a certificate of purchase as to its use is guilty. See new law Chap. 376 of Acts 1933. 

Com. v. Stevens, 153 Mass. 421

Evidence is admissible as to the number of sales a druggist makes as to near a certain date, to negative the defense that the defendant used every precaution to obey the law. 

Com. v. Perry, 148 Mass. 160

The burden of proof is on the retail druggist proceeded against for making a sale of pure alcohol to show that the sale was lawful. 

Com. v. Brown, 149 Mass. 35

If a druggist sold liquor under a certificate and knew that the purchaser did not intend to use it for medicinal purposes, but as a beverage, it is an unlawful sale of liquor. 

Com. v. Joslin, 158 Mass. 482

A druggist selling to a minor does so at his peril. If the druggist sold under a certificate knowing that it was to be used as a beverage, he is guilty; if he sold under a prescription and then used it illegally, he is not guilty. 

Com. v. Murphy, 155 Mass. 284

A complaint alleged a sale to a minor without charging delivery. It is a good complaint; the fact of it being produced to be used as evidence will not prohibit a conviction.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Stevens, 155 Mass. 291

A register kept by a druggist can be testified to orally and it need not be produced. A 6th class certificate does not allow liquor to be drunk on the premises even when purchased as a medicine. 

Com. v. Foster, 182 Mass. 276

It is a question of fact for the jury whether liquors kept in a drug store are kept legally or illegally. 

Com. v. Ramsdell, 130 Mass. 68

The keeping of intoxicating liquors by a druggist only for the purpose of mixing with other ingredients according to a prescription of physicians, to be used as a medicine and for manufacturing compounds is not a violation of law. The true test is to inquire whether the article sold is in reality an intoxicating liquor; if it is, the sale is illegal, although it is sold to be used as a medicine or an attempt is made to disguise it under the name of a medicine, or it is a mixture of liquor and other ingredients. But if the article sold cannot be used as an intoxicating liquor drink, it is not within the prohibition of the statute although it contains as one of its ingredients some spirituous liquor. 

Com. v. Gould, 158 Mass. 499

A druggist selling liquor known to be used as a beverage even if sold under a certificate is guilty. The commonwealth, however, must show and actually prove knowledge; reasonable cause is not enough. If the purchaser has a certificate stating that the liquor was for an authorized purpose and the purchaser wants it for that purpose, if the druggist sells with a guilty intent thinking that the purchaser wants it for a beverage, he is not guilty, the sale is lawful. The witness may testify that it was bought for beverage purposes on the certificate. 

Joint defendants. 

Com. v. Lynch, 160 Mass. 298

If A keeps in B's house liquor which A intends to sell, and B keeps and maintains the house, even if B did not intend to sell, both are guilty. 

Com. v. Reed, 162 Mass. 215

One may be convicted on proof that he kept the tenement, having it used by some person other than the defendant, and did not intend to make the sales himself.

CRIMINAL CASES 

81 

Com. v. Ahearn, 160 Mass. 300

Where several persons are engaged in a common criminal design with a distribution of parts and each takes a part, all are guilty. A kept the liquor in a house, B took it and sold it a mile away. A had knowledge, therefore he is guilty. 

Com. v. Burke, 114 Mass. 261

A servant may be convicted if he assist in the violation. 

Com. v. Moore, 157 Mass. 324

All persons concerned in the illegal keeping are principals. 

Com. v. McCluskey, 116 Mass. 64

One who conveys liquor to another with cause to believe that they in turn are to sell to a third person in violation of law is guilty. 

Com. v. Jennings, 107 Mass. 488

If the defendant is interested in the profits of the business, in fact a partner, he is guilty; but the defendant might be interested in the profits as a compensation for doing some lawful work and yet be not a partner, nor the keeper of the tenement charged. Overlapping. 

Com. v. Dunster, 145 Mass. 101 

See under Pleading.

APPEALS 

Amending a record or judgment in the Superior Court. 

Com. v. Le Clair, 147 Mass. 539

A complaint in the Superior Court on papers transmitted from the lower court was for a nuisance that the defendant could not have committed. The trial in the Superior Court must be for the same offense as that tried in the lower court. The proper practice is to set the verdict aside and start anew. 

Com. v. Sullivan, 138 Mass. 191

Copy of the complaint and judgment amending the complaint and judgment filed in the Superior Court. The defendant was tried on the second copy. Held that the defendant was tried in the Superior Court on the same complaint to which he pleaded in the lower court.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Foynes, 126 Mass. 267

A complaint after a verdict in the Superior Court cannot be amended without the consent of the defendant or his attorney. 

It is not the same case that he was tried on in the lower court. 

If this is done, the defendant is entitled to an acquittal. It can be amended before the case goes to the jury. 

Com. v. Vincent, 165 Mass. 18 

Com. v. Quirk, 155 Mass. 296

A judgment entered in the Superior Court can be amended at any time before the case is given to the jury. 

Com. v. Carney, 153 Mass. 444 

Com. v. Foynes, 126 Mass. 267

After a verdict in the Superior Court the defendant objected to an amendment copy of the judgment being filed. As the amended copy amended the fact that a special justice held the trial in the lower court, the amendment did not affect the rights of the defendant in that it did not change the complaint, but affected the jurisdiction of the court. 

Com. v. Kelley, 12 Gray, 123

If the copy of the complaint sent to the Superior Court varies from the original, the court may suspend the trial to enable the district attorney to procure an accurate copy, and upon obtaining it, resume the trial. 

Com. v. Magoun, 14 Gray, 398

After the evidence is all in, the papers can be amended in the Superior Court and the defendant plead anew. 

Com. v. Dressel, 110 Mass. 102

On appeal the defendant was tried on a complaint which was different so far as the date was concerned. After the verdict this was amended. Too late, it must be done before. 

Com. v. Galligan, 113 Mass. 203

The question whether the words in the copy transmitted to the Superior Court had been erased is to be determined by the Superior Court on inspection. If the complaint differed from the judgment this can be amended. 

Com. v. Wiggin, 111 Mass. 428

A trial justice sent unattested copies to the Superior Court. 

Before trial in the latter court the commonwealth can procure attested ones.

CRIMINAL CASES 

883 

Vacates the sentence appealed from. 

Com. v. Downing, 150 Mass. 197

A defendant sentenced in the lower court and committed to jail in default of bail can be tried anew in the Superior Court on the same facts, not being tried and sentenced twice for the same offense. The appeal vacates the sentence of the lower court. 

Com. v. Whalen, 147 Mass. 376 

Com. v. Kingman, 15 Gray, 208

The appeal vacates the judgment of the lower court. It renders immaterial all errors and irregularities of the proceedings there and gives the defendant full rights in the Superior Court as the whole case is opened there as to the law, the facts, and the judgment. 

Com. v. Huard, 121 Mass. 56 

Com. v. Holmes, 119 Mass. 195

The judgment shows that in the lower court the defendant was before a certain county having jurisdiction and before a court held by a trial justice. Any irregularity in the recital of the proceedings is rendered immaterial by the appeal. 

Com. v. Malloy, 119 Mass. 347 

Com. v. Loghlin, 15 Gray, 569

If the complaint in the Superior Court is sufficient to authorize a verdict and judgment upon it, the misrecital of it in the record sent up from the lower court is no grounds for dismissal. 

Com. v. Harvey, 111 Mass. 420 

Com. v. Gustin, 2 Allen, 402

Irregularities in the proceedings in the lower court presents no grounds for dismissing the complaint in the Superior Court, for the appeal vacated the judgment and gave the defendant full rights in the latter court. Objection therefore to the service of the warrant could not be made for the first time in the 

Superior Court. 

Com. v. McCormack, 7 Allen, 532 

Com. v. Calhane, 108 Mass. 431

If the judgment sent to the Superior Court is erroneous but the complaint is correct, this furnishes no grounds in the Superior Court for dismissing the complaint. The appeal voids the judgment.

CRIMINAL CASES 

After a plea of nolo contendere in lower court. 

Com. v. Mariano, 254 Mass. 533

An appeal to the Superior Court after a plea of nolo contendere in the lower court does not entitle the defendant to a trial in the latter court.

Late entries in the Superior Court. 

Com. v. McPherson, 147 Mass. 578

Certified copies can be transmitted and may be filed at any time during the term next to be held in the county, before the defendant is called to plead. 

Right of appeal, when. 

Runaldo v. Lummus, 205 Mass. 155 

Suspended sentence. The right to appeal from a sentence in the criminal case is only from the sentence after conviction. The defendant must appeal then. After the suspended sentence is revoked, it is then too late. Where jurisdiction is given to a court or magistrate by statute and there is no provision for appeal, the decision of the court or magistrate is final. 

Mariano v. Justices, 243 Mass. 90

The time for appeal from a conviction in the lower court is immediately after the imposition of the sentence. The imposition of a sentence after a finding or a plea of guilty in a district court constitutes a "conviction" as that word is used in the General Laws, Chapter 278, Section 18, giving the right of appeal to the Superior Court, and General Laws, Chapter 279, Section 1, providing for the suspension of the execution of the sentence and the placing of the defendant upon probation. 

Weiner v. Wentworth, 181 Mass. 15

The defendant was sentenced to the House of Correction. A mittimus had been issued and the defendant had been removed from the court in pursuance of the same. On the way to the prison the defendant claimed an appeal. It was too late. 

Jurisdiction. 

David v. McEnarney, 150 Mass. 451

In the Superior Court it is too late to object to the jurisdiction of the lower court. The Superior Court had acquired jurisdiction of subject and parties.

CRIMINAL CASES 

85 Must be tried in Superior Court on same complaint as in lower court. 

Com. v. Duggan, 257 Mass. 465

The defendant must be tried in the Superior Court upon the same complaint as that upon which he was tried in the lower court. The defendant was tried in the Superior Court on the certified record of a complaint dated March 9, while the copy of the complaint transmitted with the record was dated April 1.

Clerk in the lower court changed the complaint, and the defendant filed a plea in abatement. Held that the trial in the 

Superior Court was not on the same complaint. 

Com. v. Ronan, 126 Mass. 59 

Com. v. Duggan, 257 Mass. 465

In the Superior Court the conviction must be for the same offense of which he was convicted in the lower court, but not upon the same evidence. 

Withdrawal of the plea. 

Com. v. Crapo, 212 Mass. 209

An appeal from the lower court on a plea of guilty does not entitle the defendant to a trial in the Superior Court unless the justice of the latter court allows him to withdraw his plea. The defendant filed a motion but did not offer any evidence, and the court overruled it.

Seal. 

Com. v. Cavey, 97 Mass. 541 

Com. v. Bellows, 115 Mass. 139

A seal of the court is not necessary on the copies transmitted to the Superior Court on appeal. 

Com. v. Ballou, 112 Mass. 279

On trial in the Superior Court a copy of the warrant issued by a police court certified by the clerk to be a true copy and bearing the seal of the court is sufficient evidence that the criminal warrant was under seal. 

Bail and recognizance. 

Com. v. Leighton, 7 Allen, 528

One who appeals to the Superior Court and gives an illegal recognizance; the appeal is valid notwithstanding. It is a good

CRIMINAL CASES 

defense to an action on the recognizance but cannot be taken advantage of for dismissing the case. 

Com. v. Sullivan, 11 Gray, 203 

Com. v. Lynch, 14 Gray, 383

If a judgment transmitted to the Superior Court does not state whether or not the defendant entered into a recognizance, this fact is immaterial. 

Com. v. Graves, 112 Mass. 282

A prisoner was committed to jail for his failure to recognize. 

On motion of the District Attorney the court ordered that he be discharged without delay. He not being legally grieved by this discharge cannot appeal. 

Com. v. Whitney, 108 Mass. 5

One who defaults his bail on appeal waives his right to a jury trial. He then may be sentenced on his default, and this is not a violation of his constitutional rights. 

Parent can appeal for a minor. 

Robinson v. Com., 242 Mass. 401

Under General Laws, Chapter 119, Section 52, a justice may permit an appeal to be taken by the parent in behalf of a minor child. The child may withdraw his appeal, however, in the absence of the parent.

Claiming an appeal, no record being made of same. 

Willis v. Stevens, 2 Gray, 115

The defendant claimed an appeal which the court refused to allow. The record did not state nor show that he claimed an appeal, and oral testimony is not admissible to show that he did. 

Committing defendant instead of allowing appeal. 

Kendall v. Power, 4 Met. 553

Defendant was sentenced to 30 days' imprisonment. He appealed. He was not able to secure bail and the court ordered him. to be committed on the sentence instead of committing him to jail pending his securing bail. The justice was civilly liable. 

Error in the court record. 

Com. v. Welsh, 174 Mass. 327

If there was an error in the court record or in the recognizance, it affords no remedy for a motion in arrest of judgment.

CRIMINAL CASES 

87 

Judgment and complaints transmitted. 

Com. v. Munn, 156 Mass. 51

The complaint and warrant were certified, the judgment was not. Good. 

Com. v. Bisch, 145 Mass. 375

Judgment did not show that the defendant recognized by a copy of the recognizance transmitted to the Superior Court. 

Defendant appealed to the February term, 1887. The judgment showed February 1866. Whole record shows that it was a clerical error and furnished no grounds for quashing. 

Com. v. Keenan, 140 Mass. 481

Copy of the judgment means copy of the complaint. Judgment entered upon it. It was the practice to send to the Superior 

Court a copy of the complaint in order that it could be sent to the jury room, and not a copy of the judgment. 

Com. v. Parker, 140 Mass. 439

The defendant appealed to the Superior Court for his appearance on the second Monday of the month. The legislature in the meantime changed the sitting to the first Monday. The appeal was good as it was to the same court. 

Com. v. Brown, 123 Mass. 410

The fact that a judgment on an appeal to the Superior Court does not recite the fact that the case was tried at a criminal term is of no avail. The presumption is that it was tried before a criminal court, inasmuch as the criminal court sits every day. 

Com. v. Oakes, 151 Mass. 394

In a judgment sent to the Superior Court the fact that "severally" was not used in describing the pleas of the several defendants does not void the complaint. Failing to send a copy of the complaint to the Superior Court does not affect the jurisdiction of the Superior Court and furnished no grounds for an arrest of judgment. 

Com. v. Murphy, 155 Mass. 284

A judgment sets out that the defendant was brought before the court by virtue of a warrant; a copy of the warrant need not be sent to the Superior Court.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Clark, 16 Gray, 88

In copies sent to the Superior Court signed by the Clerk pro tem that fact need not be set forth nor the reason thereof. 

Com. v. Hogan, 113 Mass. 7 

Com. v. Taylor, 113 Mass. 1

The term of court was held three days in one town and three days in another. The record transmitted to the Superior Court failed to set forth the place where the court was held at the time proceedings were had, and this omission was fatal unless the same was amended before the verdict. 

Com. v. Bray, 117 Mass. 150
