On conviction and appeal it is not necessary for the district court justice to make an order to transmit the copy of the complaint and conviction to the Superior Court. The law is complied with when the clerk transmits the proceedings to the latter court. 

Com. v. Doty, 2 Met. 18

On the appeal the defendant cannot be convicted unless the justice has transmitted copies of the proceedings, and the defendant does not waive the objection by going to trial without raising it.

ARREST 

On suspicion. 

Keefe v. Hart, 213 Mass. 476

An officer who arrests a man without a warrant must take him before the court, and the court is to decide whether there is enough evidence to grant process. He cannot be held as a suspicious person; he must be charged with a crime. In this case it was held that 12 hours was too long to hold a man in the station house without charging him with a crime. He can sign a release and ask to go at large, however. 

Wax v. McGrath, 255 Mass. 340

A man was arrested on suspicion of having committed a felony and booked at the station house. After being in custody for some time he signed a waiver and was released. It is a question of fact for the jury as to whether this waiver was voluntary or not. In this case he signed the waiver under protest.

CRIMINAL CASES 

89 

Shea v. Sullivan, 261 Mass. 255

A minor was arrested on a train at the request of a passenger. The defendant was kept in custody for two days and then signed a release. A minor can waive his right.

When a person is under arrest. 

Whitehead v. Keyes, 3 Allen, 501

An officer arrests a person when he has the authority to arrest by laying his hand on him for the purpose of arresting him, though he may not succeed in stopping or holding him. 

Com. v. Merrick, 255 Mass. 510

A man accompanies an officer to the station house without compulsion, on the statement that the chief wants to see him; does not constitute an arrest. Statements made to the officer en route are admissible. 

Com. v. Morihan, 4 Allen, 585

An officer's custody of a prisoner whom he has arrested upon a warrant and brought before the court does not cease until the prisoner has been discharged or a warrant of commitment has been made out. A prisoner escaped from the court after sentence had been pronounced and one who aided in his escape could be convicted of aiding in the escape from an officer. 

Powers v. Sturtevant, 199 Mass. 265

An officer in making an arrest is authorized to use such force as is necessary to overcome resistance, but if he uses excessive force he is liable, and it is no defense that he acted without malice, and in the honest belief that the force he used was necessary. 

Rearrest after letting prisoner go at large. 

Com. v. Hastings, 9 Met. 259

A police officer has all the powers of a constable excepting the service of civil process. He arrests an intoxicated person while guilty of disorderly conduct and then releases him on his promise to go directly home. He may lawfully retake him on his going into a bar room before he is out of the officer's sight. A special officer has the authority to act outside of the theater in which he acts that is in the vicinity thereof.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Need not exhibit when — warrant. 

Com. v. Cooley, 6 Gray, 350 

Com. v. Field, 13 Mass. 321

If the prisoner resists the officer in making the arrest the officer is not bound to exhibit his warrant and read it to him, if the party submits to the arrest otherwise. 

Right to arrest. 

Sullivan v. Wentworth, 137 Mass. 233 

Com. v. Tobin, 108 Mass. 426 

Com. v. Wright, 158 Mass. 158 

Creedon v. B. & M. Ry., 193 Mass. 280

A constable has the right by virtue of his office without a warrant to enter through an unfastened door in which there is a disturbance of the peace and arrest any persons disturbing the peace in his presence. 

By a citizen. 

Jacques v. Childs Dining Room, 244 Mass. 438

The right of a private citizen to arrest without a warrant is confined to cases of actual guilt of the parties arrested, and the arrest can be justified only by proving such guilt. Officer. 

Wax v. McGrath, 255 Mass. 340 

Com. v. Wright, 158 Mass. 158

A police officer having reasonable cause to suspect one guilty of a felony may arrest without a warrant. 

Railroad police officer. 

Hull v. B. & M. Ry., 210 Mass. 159

Railroad officers can arrest only for evasion of fare and for disorderly conduct on the train. 

Arrest in a house. 

Ford v. Breen, 173 Mass. 52

An officer can arrest without a warrant for drunkenness, and can enter through an open door without an invitation.

CRIMINAL CASES 

91 

Com. v. Reynolds, 120 Mass. 190 

Bernard v. Bartlett, 10 Cush. 501

An officer who holds a warrant for the arrest of a man for a misdemeanor can enter a house if he has reasonable cause to suspect that the man wanted is in there, after notifying the owner that he has such process; and on refusal can enter the outer door by force; and the owner is liable for assault if he resists. 

The officer need not notify the owner whom he seeks. 

Com. v. Irwin, 1 Allen, 587

If an officer has a warrant, after demanding admittance he can break doors. A warrant for B, he can enter the house of C and need not exhibit the warrant to the householder provided the latter has reasonable notice that the officer is in fact an officer and acting under a warrant for the person supposed to be there. 

Com. v. McGahey, 77 Mass. 194

A constable endeavoring to rearrest a prisoner who has escaped, after demand and refusal of admission may break doors. 

Com. v. Shedd, 13 Mass. 520

A dwelling house is a protection from arrest on a civil process to the occupant, children and domestic servants, permanent boarders and lodgers, but not to strangers or visitors. 

Com. v. Phelps, 209 Mass. 396

An officer has the right to arrest without a warrant because he suspects on reasonable ground that the defendant has committed a felony, and he has the right to break open doors. If the defendant, without warning or notice to the officer to desist, in cold blood and with malice kills the officer, he is guilty of murder, even though the officer is acting without right in making the arrest without a warrant. 

Arrest without a warrant. 

Com. v. Wright, 158 Mass. 158

Collection of cases and arrests without a warrant discussed and explained. 

Rohan v. Sawin, 59 Mass. 281

A person may be lawfully arrested by a constable or peace officer without a warrant upon reasonable ground to believe such person has committed a felony. He cannot arrest without a warrant one who is suspected of having committed an offense unless such offense amounts to a felony.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Tobin, 108 Mass. 426

A peace officer may arrest without a warrant for breaches of the peace committed in his presence. 

Arrest on a conveyance. 

Krulevitz v. Eastern Ry., 143 Mass. 228

The conductor of a train caused an arrest to be made of a passenger who refused to pay his fare by an officer who did not witness it. The conductor should have arrested the passenger himself, and the arrest was illegal. 

Hull v. B. & M. Ry., 210 Mass. 159

Railroad police can arrest only for evading fare and for disorderly conduct on a train. 

Reasonable belief. 

Robinson v. Van Auken, 190 Mass. 167

The legality or the illegality of an arrest depends on whether the officer has reasonable cause to believe the defendant guilty of a felony. The defendant was charged with larceny of $74, which is not a felony.

Burden of proof on arrest. 

Jackson v. Knowlton, 173 Mass. 95

In an action for arrest the burden of proof is on the officer to prove justification. 

Short v. Symmes, 150 Mass. 298

A person who assumes to arrest another person for a crime and attempts to justify such arrest as properly done must show not merely that he was an officer de facto but that he was legally qualified to act as such.

Release of prisoner after arrest. 

Joyce v. Parkhurst, 150 Mass. 243 

Caffrey v. Duggan, 144 Mass. 294

An officer arrested A for drunkenness while committing a breach of the peace. He begged to be allowed to go, and the officer did not make any complaint against him. He had waived his rights, and no grounds existed for false arrest and imprisonment.

CRIMINAL CASES 

93 

Bates v. Reynolds, 195 Mass. 549

A prisoner taken to the station house was allowed to return home if he desired. He insisted on remaining there and he was permitted to do so. He was sent home the next morning in a carriage. He had an amicable agreement with the officer and was barred from bringing suit.

As to be taken before the court after arrest. 

Hackett v. King, 6 Allen, 58

A person arrested upon a warrant and without being taken before a magistrate and the warrant returned was taken to the officer's house, there executed a release, and was thereupon released from custody. Such an arrest is false imprisonment and against all who procured or participated in the same. The release given under such circumstances is void. 

Com. v. Gorman, 288 Mass. 294

The fact that the complainant did not make a return on the complaint under Section 34 of Chapter 218 of the General Laws does not entitle the defendant to any rights. In this case he was arrested without a warrant for operating under the influence, and no warrant was afterwards issued. He gave bail for his appearance before the court. The fact that the defendant was physically before the court is enough and he can be tried. The return stating the fact that he was arrested is not essential. 

Illegal arrest. 

Com. v. Tay, 170 Mass. 192 

Com. v. Conlin, 184 Mass. 195

One arrested for a crime illegally is brought before the court, and notwithstanding the illegality of the arrest, the court has jurisdiction over the person of the defendant and can proceed to try him. The defendant, however, has a civil liability for the illegal arrest. 

Com. v. Carey, 12 Cush. 246 

Com. v. Drew, 4 Mass. 391 

Com. v. Phelps, 209 Mass. 396

If a person resisting an illegal arrest takes the life of the person arresting him, he is not guilty of murder but of manslaughter. 

A constable cannot without a warrant arrest a person for a past offense unless such offense amounts to a felony.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Pearce v. Atwood, 13 Mass. 324

An arrest by an officer on a void and unlawful process is illegal. The officer making the arrest is a trespasser. 

Donahue v. Shedd, 8 Met. 326

An action will not lie against an officer serving a warrant issued in legal form by a court having jurisdiction and directing him to arrest a party, even though the proceedings of the court issuing the warrant may have been erroneous. 

Drunkenness. See also title of Drunkenness. 

Com. v. Conlin, 184 Mass. 195

An illegal arrest for drunkenness cannot avail the defendant. 

He is before the court and the court has jurisdiction over his person. 

Horgan v. B. E. Ry., 208 Mass. 287 

One arrested for drunkenness asks for and signs a release under the statute; he cannot afterwards sue the officer. 

Eldredge v. Mitchell, 214 Mass. 480 

Cannot arrest a person for drunkenness without a warrant unless the person arrested is drunk at the time of the arrest. 

Sullivan v. O. C. Ry., 148 Mass. 119 

Conductors who are railroad officers may remove a passenger who is drunk and disorderly and place him in the baggage car, using reasonable force. It is not necessary to arrest him. 

Com. v. Kennedy, 136 Mass. 152

The defendant while drunk entered a passenger car. He was ordered to leave by the conductor; he did so and returned. The officer was called and ordered the defendant out; the defendant refused, and the officer arrested him for drunkenness. Subsequently a complaint for assault and battery on an officer was granted. The arrest for drunkenness was legal. 

Com. v. Cheney, 141 Mass. 102

A police officer who arrests one for drunkenness is not liable civilly if he acted and had reasonable cause to believe the defendant was drunk, even if in fact he was not so.

CRIMINAL CASES 

95 

Watch and ward statute.

GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 41, SEC. 98 

Creedon v. B. & M. Ry., 193 Mass. 280

A passenger who is a passenger on a train is not "abroad" in the sense of liability to arrest under the watch and ward statute. 

Fugitive from justice.

Scott v. Eldredge, 154 Mass. 25 

A peace officer is not authorized to arrest in this state without a warrant for a past misdemeanor committed in another state. The defendant was arrested on information contained in a letter sent from Pennsylvania requesting an arrest. No indictment was sent.

Warrants must be directed specially to officers. 

Graves Case, 236 Mass. 493

A warrant must contain and it must be directed to some officer or class of officers, not to a department or organization or unofficial body. 

Insane person. 

Look v. Dean, 108 Mass. 116

The mere fact that a man is insane does not authorize his arrest and detention without a warrant, if he is not dangerous to himself or others. The fact that two physicians signed the commitment is not good, for they did not assert that he was insane. All who are connected with his arrest and detention are liable. 

Past threats.

Eldredge v. Mitchell, 214 Mass. 480 

Cannot arrest upon information that assault and threats to kill were made by such person the day before. Cannot arrest a person without a warrant because the officer believes him to be a dangerous person to be at large. 

Arrest in apprehension of a crime. 

Com. v. Tracey, 5 Met. at 550

The commonwealth is not bound to wait until its own laws are violated. Reasonable apprehension of danger is sufficient

CRIMINAL CASES 

to justify a preliminary interposition to prevent it. Analogous to cases of persons who by their language or conduct have shown themselves to be dangerous.

John Doe warrant. 

Com. v. Crotty, 10 Allen, 403

A warrant for the arrest of John Doe without any description other than the true name is void. This has now been changed by statute, General Laws, Chapter 277, Section 19. 

Verbal order to arrest. 

Com. v. McGahey, 77 Mass. 194

A prisoner may be arrested by a constable without a warrant by a verbal order of a justice of the peace for an assault committed in his presence. 

Lord's day gaming. 

McLennon v. Richardson, 81 Mass. 77

A constable has no authority without a warrant or previous demand to forcibly enter a shop at midnight preceding the Lord's Day and arrest the owner and others present for gaming. 

Must return property of prisoner. 

King v. Ham, 6 Allen, 298

A warrant to arrest for larceny and take the property from the prisoner and hold the same is limited to the articles alleged to have been taken and stolen, and an officer who takes and holds a promissory note taken from the prisoner and holds the same for two years is liable if the maker of the note becomes insolvent and cannot pay. 

Warrant issued by a legislative body. 

Sanborn v. Carlton, 15 Gray, 399

A warrant issued by the United States Senate for a contempt witness and addressed to the sergeant at arms cannot be served by a deputy in this state. 

Com. v. Morrissey, 14 Gray, 226

The sergeant at arms of the state legislature may detain in the county jail a prisoner committed by authority of the House of Representatives.

CRIMINAL CASES 

97 

Service of a search warrant. 

Leddy v. Grossman, 108 Mass. 237

The officer was legally serving a search warrant in the premises of the defendant when he assaulted the officer, who thereupon arrested him without a warrant. The obstructing being a part of the whole transaction, the officer had the right to arrest the defendant either during the disturbance or immediately afterwards.

After pleading cannot raise question of arrest. 

McCabe v. Dowd, 7 Allen, 477

After the defendant appears in court according to the tenor of his recognizance and pleads not guilty and puts himself on trial, it is then too late for him to object for the first time that he was not treated rightfully upon his arrest. 

One deputized entitled to protection. 

Com. v. Field, 13 Mass. 321

One appointed by a sheriff to aid him in the service of an execution is entitled equally with the sheriff to the protection of the law. The deputization need not be in writing. Pleading.

ARSON 

ACTS OF 1932, CHAP. 192 GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 266 

Com. v. Cooper, 264 Mass. 378

The omission of the words "maliciously and wilfully" is fatal and is not cured by the provisions of Chapter 277, Section 33, of the General Laws. 

Com. v. Mahar, 33 Mass. 120

The name of the owner of the dwelling house must be averred. 

Com. v. Hamilton, 15 Gray, 480

An indictment for arson need not contain an averment of its value unless the punishment depends on the value. 

Com. v. Flynn, 3 Cush. at 529

In an averment for attempt to burn it is not necessary to describe the combustibles used for that purpose.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Squire, 1 Met. 258 

Com. v. Tucker, 110 Mass. 403

The term "building" need not represent a finished structure and perfect for the purpose for which it was intended to be used. 

Com. v. Horrigan, 2 Allen, 159

A building which was removed and afterwards fitted up as a schoolhouse and engine house will support an allegation that the building was erected for the public use. The removal of a building and the fitting up for occupancy is "erecting.' 

Com. v. Barney, 10 Cush. 478 

It must be a dwelling house for the habitation of man. 

Com. v. Jaffas, 284 Mass. 417

The indictment read "did willfully and maliciously aid, counsel and assist in an attempt, etc," to set fire to or burn a building. It was not defective in that it omitted the words "willfully and maliciously" as a description of the attempt to set fire to or burn. The words "did counsel, aid and assist" were superfluous and unnecessary, the crime being a misdemeanor, and might be rejected as surplusage. The gravamen of the offense is that the defendant participated, and the fact that he was only a part owner was not a defense. 

Com. v. Uhrig, 167 Mass. 420 

Com. v. Prece, 140 Mass. 276

Form for burning a warehouse. An allegation "in the night time" could be rejected as surplusage. 

What constitutes a burning? 

Com. v. Van Schack, 16 Mass. 105

If any part of a dwelling house however small be willfully and maliciously consumed by fire, the crime is complete. A coal of fire was placed on the window sill and burned only six inches of space. Crime completed. Evidence. 

Com. v. Vellucci, 284 Mass. 443

The mere circumstance that the wife of the defendant and not the defendant himself was the insured, and the lack of evidence that the house was overinsured do not render unwarranted a verdict of guilty.

CRIMINAL CASES 

99 

Com. v. Mehales, 284 Mass. 412

The defendant placed gasoline and made a bomb. He placed these in a certain house but he did not ignite them, light any match, or set any fire. An attempt was made out under Sec tion 5 of Chapter 192 of the Acts of 1932. Malice under Section 5 

is not necessarily against the owner of the building but that malice which characterizes an act done with evil disposition, a wrong and unlawful motive or purpose; the willful doing of an injurious act without lawful excuse. 

Com. v. Bader, 285 Mass. 574

The complaint was for aiding, counseling and procuring the burning of his own building willfully and with intent to defraud. insurers, Acts of 1932, Chapter 192. The defendant was without income save $20 weekly; took title to a house, paid nothing down, took policy from the bank for $6500, procured an additional policy of insurance of $5000, for which he paid nothing. He possessed the only key to the house. When the firemen arrived they found no trace of incendiary materials, the electricity was shut off, and the house was void of tenancy. There was not any evidence that the defendant was about or near the premises at the time of the fire, and the commonwealth did not contend that he set it. No other person had any motive. The court decided that there was enough evidence to convict. 

Com. v. Hayden, 150 Mass. 332

If the complaint alleges the burning of a dwelling house, it must be proved as laid; burning a building is not sufficient. 

Com. v. Leventhal, 236 Mass. 516

Where the evidence charged the burning of a dwelling house of A and tends only to show the burning of a dwelling house owned by A but not dwelt in by A, there is no variance. 

Com. v. Makeley, 131 Mass. 421

An indictment of the burning of a dwelling house of another is not sustained by proof that the defendant burned the house by the owner's procurement to enable him to obtain money from the insurer. 

Com. v. Brailey, 134 Mass. 527

The complaint charged the burning of the building of another and of property contained therein of a value of $1000, the

CRIMINAL CASES 

property of P. The evidence showed that it was the property of 

P, but the property contained therein was the property of B. 

The crime was the burning of the building, and the variance was not fatal. 

Com. v. Bradford, 126 Mass. 42 

Com. v. Hudson, 97 Mass. 565

A conversation with an insurance man relative to adding additional insurance by a man thirty days previous to his setting the fire is admissible. 

Com. v. Cooper, 264 Mass. 368

The complaint was for burning insured property with intent to defraud an insurance company. It is enough if inferences are not too remote according to the usual course of events, and if all the circumstances including inferences are of sufficient force to bring minds of ordinary intelligence and sagacity to the pursuasion of incendiarism beyond a reasonable doubt. Financial resources of the defendant must be shown by direct evidence, and they are not proved by general reputation or by abstract assertions that his business was good or even has been profitable for a full period. 

Com. v. Alba, 271 Mass. 333 

Com. v. Selesnick, 272 Mass. 354

In a complaint for accessory the commonwealth must show and prove that the principal burned the building with the intent to injure the insurer. A direct benefit to the defendant is not made a necessary element of the crime. 

Com. v. Goldstein, 114 Mass. 272

Intent to injure insurers: if the goods are covered by two policies it is only one crime, and the act is the burning with the intent to defraud. 

Com. v. Goodwin, 14 Gray, 55

Evidence of threats of revenge against the owner of the building, uttered by the defendant from one to two years previous to the fire, are admissible. 

Com. v. Cooper, 264 Mass. 368

Common law arson is the malicious and willful burning of another house or outhouse.

CRIMINAL CASES 

101 

ASSAULT AND BATTERY COMMON LAW CRIME. See GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 279, SEC. 5. 

Definition of. 

Com. v. McKie, 1 Gray, 61 

Com. v. Ruggles, 6 Allen, 588

An assault and battery consists in the unlawful and unjustifiable use of force and violence upon the person of another, however slight. 

Assault without touching. 

Com. v. Wing, 9 Pick. 1

If one discharges a gun at wild fowl with the knowledge and warning that the report will injuriously affect the health of a sick person in the neighborhood and such effect is produced, he is guilty of an indictable crime. Form for the same. 

Com. v. Hurley, 99 Mass. 433

Evidence that one of a noisy crowd near a police officer cried "Kill the son of a bitch" about the time when others knocked the officer to the ground is sufficient to warrant a conviction notwithstanding he did not in person touch or injure the officer. 

Com. v. Hagenblock, 140 Mass. 125

The defendant got into a bed where a woman was lying, removing the clothes without touching her. She stepped over him to get out. He put out his hand to stop her but did not touch her. Guilty of assault. 

Reasonable force. 

Com. v. Coffey, 121 Mass. 66

The daughter of the defendant was living apart from him. 

She was taken sick and her father desired her to return home with him. She refused, and in company with others he removed her. No undue force was used, but the jury could well find them guilty in that considering her illness undue force was used. 

Powers v. Sturtevant, 199 Mass. 365

An officer making an arrest for assault and battery is authorized to use reasonable force, but if he uses excessive force he is liable, and it is no defense that he acted without malice and in an honest belief that the force used was necessary.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Malone, 114 Mass. 295

A mother may use reasonable force to protect her daughter from a violent and indecent assault committed by a man, even if the daughter is sixteen years old; and the use of such force does not justify the man in striking the mother. 

Churchill v. Hurlbut, 110 Mass. 42

One who has an irrevocable license to enter one's premises can so enter if he does so peacefully and without opposition, but if he resisted he cannot resort to personal violence to enforce his rights. 

Retaking one's property. 

Com. v. Donahue, 148 Mass. 529

One whose property is taken unlawfully and wrongly by another party may thereupon retake it from him, using no more than reasonable force. What is force is a jury question. 

Sampson v. Henry, 11 Pick. 379 

Iseln v. Nichols, 12 Pick. 270

Officers serving a writ. The law does not allow anyone to break the peace forcibly to redress his private wrongs. He may make use of force to defend his lawful possession but being dispossessed, he has no right to recover possession by force and by a breach of the peace. Justification. 

Com. v. Goodwin, 3 Cush. 154

What amounts to justification is a question of fact for the jury.

Provocation. 

Dupee v. Lentine, 147 Mass. 580

Provocation cannot be shown unless it is so recent and immediate as to form part of the provocation. 

Ejecting guests and tenants. 

Holden v. Carragher, 195 Mass. 392

An innkeeper has the right to eject guests who are acting improperly. 

Stone v. Leahy, 133 Mass. 426

If a tenant at will surrenders the premises under an agreement with the owner and leaves behind a person who has occu-

CRIMINAL CASES 

103 pied the premises with him, but without the owner's knowledge or consent, the owner is not liable for an action of assault and battery if he ejects him after a refusal to leave. 

Com. v. Haley, 4 Allen, 318 

Meader v. Stone, 7 Met. 147

A landlord having the right of immediate possession may take it without legal process if he does so peacefully, but he has no right to forcibly remove his tenant's goods or to commit an assault upon him in doing so. The landlord took off the doors and windows where there was sickness but he could not eject the tenant by actual force.

Ejecting a trespasser. 

Com. v. Clark, 2 Met. 23

In rightfully ejecting a trespasser it is for the jury to determine just how much force is used. 

Com. v. Lakeman, 4 Cush. 597

One tenant in common of a barn floor has no right to use force and violence to prevent his cotenant from entering the door leading to the floor, though such entry is with the declared purpose of removing the wagon of the former from the floor. 

Com. v. Power, 7 Met. 596

An innkeeper who has been ordered to desist from a railroad station entered there and purchased a ticket and became a bona fide passenger. The superintendent believed that he had entered to solicit trade, and he not producing the ticket, was ejected by the superintendent, who did not use unreasonable force. 

Superintendent found guilty. 

Ordering without authority. 

Com. v. Ruggles, 6 Allen, 588

An officer of a society has no power to order persons off a public highway where an exhibition was being held in connection with a fair; and a constable may be arrested for assault and battery if without legal process he arrests a person who is on the highway, who when directed to move refused so to do, and who struck the horse of the marshal of the society without malice. 

Service of process. 

Com. v. Martin, 105 Mass. 178

An officer who is serving a void search warrant can be ejected from the premises if only legal force is used.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Kennard, 8 Pick. 133

If an officer takes the goods of Smith and in Smith's possession upon a writ against Jones, Smith may maintain his possession by force, in the same manner as he might against any trespasser who is not an officer. 

Pointing a pistol. 

Com. v. White, 110 Mass. 407

If A menacingly points a gun at B which B has reasonable cause to believe is loaded and is put in fear thereby, A is guilty of an assault, although he (A) knows that the gun was unloaded. 

Com. v. McLaughlin, 5 Allen, 507

One who fires a loaded and capped pistol at another recklessly, not knowing or seeking to know whether it was loaded or not, may be convicted of assault and battery. 

Com. v. Mann, 116 Mass. 58

If a man fires a pistol at another to frighten him, he does so at his peril, even if he does not intend to hit him, and he is guilty of assault and battery. But if he believes he is in danger of his life and does so with the intention of frightening him, this does not apply, but in either case it is a question for the jury. 

Mutual consent. 

Com. v. Collberg, 119 Mass. 350

Where two persons go out to fight with their fists with mutual consent and do fight each other, they are each guilty of assault and battery although there is no anger or ill will. 

Arming oneself in apprehension of danger. 

Com. v. Crowley, 165 Mass. 569

There must be actual apprehension of an assault which would cause great bodily harm, as well as reasonable cause to apprehend such an assault, to justify arming oneself with a weapon to repel the same. 

Past threats. 

Eldredge v. Mitchell, 214 Mass. 480

An officer cannot arrest without a warrant upon information that assaults and threats to kill were made by a person on the day before.

CRIMINAL CASES 

105 

On a police officer. 

Com. v. Hirsch, 116 Mass. 349

The allegation of being a police officer does not affect the jurisdiction of the lower court. 

Com. v. Kirby, 2 Cush. at 581

The pleader must allege that the defendant knew that he was a police officer. 

Com. v. Cooley, 6 Gray, 350

Persons interfering with an arrest, not knowing that he is an officer but interfering with the intention of stopping a fight; if more force is used than is necessary, then they are guilty. 

Aiding an officer. 

Com. v. Field, 13 Mass. 321

A sheriff may appoint an aid even though it is not in writing, and an offense of assault and battery will lie. The aid is entitled to equal protection with the sheriff even though not in sight. 

Forcible entry and detainer. 

Com. v. Shattuck, 4 Cush. 141

Crime at common law. The entry must be made as to excite terror, apparent violence, show of weapons. 

Convicted of a lesser offense. 

Com. v. Crowley, 167 Mass. 434

Assault and battery with intent to commit larceny without more of a description of what was intended to be stolen is not a crime by the statute or the common law. The defendant may be convicted of assault and battery although acquitted of the full crime. 

Com. v. Clark, 162 Mass. 494 

Assault and battery with intent to kill and murder is not bad for duplicity. The defendant may be acquitted of part and convicted of the residue. 

Com. v. Delahan, 148 Mass. 254

The complaint charged an assault and battery on an officer with a weapon dangerous to life, and with obstructing and hin-

CRIMINAL CASES 

dering him in the discharge of his duty. The allegation as to the dangerous weapon can be rejected as surplusage. 

Criminal and civil cases pending. 

Com. v. Elliott, 2 Mass. 372

If an indictment and a civil action are pending at the same time, the court will not stay the proceedings on the indictment if the injured party is not to be a witness for the commonwealth. Pleading. 

Com. v. O'Brien, 107 Mass. 208

An indictment in a single count charging an assault on two persons at the same time is good, and may be supported by proof of assault upon one only. 

Com. v. Ramey, 243 Mass. 394

Evidence was admitted that two years previous the defendant had assaulted the complainant and had uttered threats shortly after. Admissible to show the state of mind. 

Com. v. Bush, 112 Mass. 280

A complaint was dated September 2 charging an assault on the same day. The complainant in his testimony stated that it occurred on the day previous. The jury might find that it did occur on the day previous and before the issuance of the warrant. 

Evidence. 

Com. v. Adams, 114 Mass. 323

One who drives negligently over another person is not guilty of a criminal assault and battery, though he does it while violating a fast-driving ordinance. 

Com. v. Beals, 133 Mass. 396

A parent to whom the custody of a child has been awarded by the probate court may be convicted of an assault and battery if in trying to force an entrance to the house in which the child has been placed for the purpose of obtaining the child, he uses intentional force upon the occupant of the house in order to overcome resistance to his entrance. 

Com. v. Randall, 4 Gray, 36

A teacher has the right to punish a pupil, but if the punishment is excessive and improper, the defendant is guilty, and

CRIMINAL CASES 

107 whether under all the facts whether the punishment is excessive is for the jury. 

Cooper v. McKenna, 124 Mass. 284

A priest has no legal authority by virtue of his priestly character to forcibly remove from a room a person lawfully there, although the priest is about to administer an office of his religion to a sick person at the request of the latter. 

Com. v. Middleby, 187 Mass. 342

It is no defense to an assault and battery that the defendant was acting under the advice of counsel. The silent approval of an assault and battery is not a crime. Sentence. 

O'Neil v. Com., 165 Mass. 446

The Superior Court may punish to three years' imprisonment in the House of Correction on an action commenced there, and is not barred by the provisions of Chapter 279, Section 5. 

ATTEMPTS GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 274, SEC. 6 

Com. v. McDonald, 5 Cush. 365

To attempt is to make an effort to effect some object, to make an experiment, to endeavor, to use an exertion to some purpose. 

Com. v. Peaslee, 177 Mass. 267 

Preparing combustibles to be lighted. The defendant offered a man a reward to light them, the man refused; the defendant then drove in to start the fire himself but within a quarter of a mile from them, he desisted and changed his mind. Held that this was an attempt. 

Com. v. Cline, 213 Mass. 225

Attempt to commit larceny from person. The pleader need not allege that there was any property in the pocket. 

Com. v. Crowley, 167 Mass. 434

A charge of attempt must be set out in direct terms. 

Com. v. Koslowsky, 238 Mass. 379

Attempt to steal an automobile. The short form upheld. The cure is only by a bill of particulars.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Jacobs, 9 Allen, 274

Soliciting a person to leave the state to enlist in the military service elsewhere is not defeated by the fact that he could not enlist. 

Com. v. Harney, 10 Met. 422

An indictment for an attempt to set fire to a building is not bad for duplicity, although it sets forth a breaking and entering of the building as well as an attempt in the building after the breach and entry to set fire to it. 

Com. v. Flynn, 3 Cush. 529

It is not necessary in an indictment for an attempt to commit a crime that it should be directly charged, that the act attempted was a crime punishable by law, provided it appeared to be so from the facts alleged. 

Com. v. Dennis, 105 Mass. 162

An attempt to commit suicide is not indictable in this commonwealth. 

Com. v. Sherman, 105 Mass. 169

To commit larceny from the person an averment that the defendant did attempt to steal from the person of A the money of said A, being in the possession and upon the person of A, and in such attempt, and with intent to commit larceny from the person of the said A, did thrust, insert, and place his hand into the pocket of said A, sufficiently describes that the pocket was a pocket in clothing which A was wearing at the time of the attempt.

McLaughlins Case, 107 Mass. 225 

Under the third paragraph in the attempt punishment a defendant may be sentenced to the state prison. 

Com. v. Lucy Ann Mink, 123 Mass. 422

By law of this state suicide is deemed criminal as malun in se, and although an attempt to commit it is not punishable, yet a person who, in attempting to commit it, accidentally kills another person who is trying to prevent it is guilty of criminal homicide. 

Com. v. Roosnel, 143 Mass. 32

An indictment for assault on a female child under the age of ten years with intent to unlawfully and carnally know and abuse may be maintained under the statute which provides that

CRIMINAL CASES 

109 "whoever assaults a female with intent to commit a rape shall be punished, etc.," although the child makes no resistance. 

Com. v. O'Neil, 188 Mass. 330

One guilty of attempted larceny from the person may be sentenced to imprisonment not exceeding 22 years, while for the completed crime of larceny from the person the imprisonment was not exceeding 2 years.

ATTORNEY AT LAW GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 221, SEC. 372 

Com. v. Dyer, 243 Mass. 472

There is no privilege between client and attorney where their conference concerned the proposed commission of crimes by the client. 

Kenney v. Driscoll, 1 Allen, 210

The appearance in the Superior Court of the magistrate who granted process on the case in the lower court is irregular and improper, but not illegal. 

Brown v. Phelps, 211 Mass. 376

One who is a member of the bar in one state cannot practice law in this state unless he is admitted to the bar in this commonwealth under the law. 

Clearing House v. Banwert, 227 Mass. 579

A corporation organized to practice law in another state cannot practice in this commonwealth. 

In re De Propper, 236 Mass. 500

Disbarment of an attorney is a matter of law and not of equity.

Jordan v. Smith, 19 Pick. 287

A constable acting as an auctioneer, without a license, of attached goods is not guilty of fraud and deceit, but is of selling without a license.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Harnden, 19 Pick. 482

An auctioneer cannot delegate his authority, but he may employ another to use the hammer, and his occasional absence during the sale will not subject his servant or his substitute to selling. It is a question for the jury to say whether this was a cover to evade the statute. 

What is operation?

AUTOMOBILE GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 90 

Com. v. Henry, 229 Mass. 19 

Jenkins v. No. Shore Dye Co., 277 Mass. 440

One who drives an automobile and leaves it standing in the street is operating it. The operation includes at least the ordinary stops on the highway, and such stops are to be regarded as fairly incidental to its operation. 

Com. v. Uski, 263 Mass. 22 

Com. v. Clark, 254 Mass. 566

The car was parked at the curb. The defendant came out of the house and entered the car, turned on the lights, and started the motor. The car moved a few feet. Whoever operates, etc., includes the setting in motion of the operative machinery of the vehicle, as well as the driving of the vehicle under the powers of the motor machinery. 

Com. v. Crowinshield, 187 Mass. 221

One who operates an automobile is driving the same. The operator controls the power. 

Com. v. Sherman, 191 Mass. 439

The owner may be convicted of operating an automobile even if he is not actually driving it. He is guilty of managing and controlling the operation of it. 

Norcross v. Roberts Co., 239 Mass. 596

Walking and pushing a disabled motor cycle which was unregistered is not operating the same on the highway. Neither is it allowing it to remain on the highway. 

Griffin v. Heustis, 234 Mass. 95

The owner of an automobile licensed merely as an operator is not authorized to operate the same for hire.

CRIMINAL CASES 

111 

Com. v. Sabean, 275 Mass. 546

The defendant was in the rear seat drunk. The operator was also drunk and was driving under the license of the man in the rear seat. The rear seat man is guilty under the provisions of 

Section 10 of Chapter 90 of the General Laws. 

Foley v. Hurley, 288 Mass. 354

When the owner of an automobile is riding in it while another is driving, in the absence of controlling evidence to the contrary, the inference is warranted that the owner retained the right to control its operation. 

Operating while under the influence of liquor. 

Com. v. Gorman, 288 Mass. 294

One operating under may be arrested without a warrant, the offense being a breach of the peace committed in the sight and view of the officer. 

Com. v. Bridges, 285 Mass. 572

Notwithstanding the fact that the defendant had been drinking 3.2 beer, in fact the same being defined as nonintoxicating; if in fact he becomes influenced thereby, he can be found guilty of operating under the influence of intoxicating liquors so far as concerns Section 24 of Chapter 90. Any liquor is intoxicating if in fact it is capable of subjecting a person to its alcoholic influence. 

Com. v. Dale, 264 Mass. 535

The commonwealth must show and prove that the intoxication was caused by the voluntary use of intoxicating liquor. 

Com. v. Lyseth, 250 Mass. 555

It is not necessary for the commonwealth to show that the defendant was drunk. He can be convicted even though there were not any travelers on the highway, and the defendant was using due care. It is not an essential element that defendant shall have failed to exercise due care to avoid injury; the test is, had the liquor he had taken influenced him in any perceptible degree. 

Com. v. Clarke, 254 Mass. 566 

Com. v. Uski, 263 Mass. 22

The defendant may be found guilty of operating even though the engine is not running. The defendant acknowledged he was drunk, and realizing it, got into the car to lock it, and in order

CRIMINAL CASES 

to do so released the brakes; the car, being on an incline, moved forward four feet, which the court decided was operating. 

Com. v. Durkin, 257 Mass. 426

Immediately after the accident the defendant was found under the influence of intoxicating liquor. One who was riding in the back seat was drunk. Another occupant was killed. An autopsy showed evidence of drinking and a bottle of liquor was found in the pocket of the dead man. Evidence may be competent if it is in the aid and incidental to other evidence (circumstantial) although it is not in and of itself evidence of the crime charged. 

Com. v. Wood, 261 Mass. 458

In the afternoon there was a crash of automobiles colliding where the traffic was stopped. Immediately afterward the defendant was found slumped down in a position behind the wheel alone. The automobile was not in motion and the motor was stopped. The car was the last of four automobiles in the street. 

Enough to convict. 

Com. v. Barney, 258 Mass. 609

Where at the trial in the Superior Court of a complaint charging a second offense there was evidence of but one offense, but no evidence to support that part of the complaint which charged a second offense, it was proper for the court to instruct the jury to disregard that part of the complaint and to consider the case as a charge for a first offense. 

Com. v. Fortier, 258 Mass. 99

At a trial in the Superior Court charging an operating under the influence of intoxicating liquor, and second offense, the defendant testifying in his own behalf may be asked on cross examination whether he was before this trial before another tribunal charged with the same offense, whether he pleaded guilty and paid a fine. His affirmative answer to each question is evidence warranting a conviction for a second offense. It was not necessary to produce the record of the court showing such previous conviction.

Operating automobile so as to endanger lives and safety, etc. 

Com. v. Mara, 257 Mass. 198

A charge of "operating so that the lives and safety" held proper even if the statute says "lives or safety."

CRIMINAL CASES 

113 

Com. v. Horsfall, 213 Mass. 232

One can be guilty of reckless driving though no one was on the street; antecedent conduct may be found to be reckless, even if all possible care may be exercised after the specific danger is actually discovered. 

Com. v. Pentz, 247 Mass. 500

The form of the complaint and the law is constitutional. Under the above case the commonwealth did not have to show intent. 

The law has now been changed, and the word "negligently" is placed therein. Chapter 90, Section 24. 

Com. v. Vartanian, 251 Mass. 355

The performance of the specific act constitutes the crime. Now negligence must be proven. 

Com. v. Leone, 250 Mass. 512

There was evidence tending to show that the defendant was approaching a square formed by the confluence of five streets and when 400 feet therefrom was running at a speed of 20 miles an hour; that he passed to the left of a trolley car going in the same direction, at the rate of nine miles an hour; that he immediately returned to the right-hand side of the road proceeding to the square; that a woman crossing the square 25 feet from the curb stopped, then moved back 15 feet and looked at an automobile approaching from the opposite direction to that from which the defendant was approaching, but did not look towards the defendant; that the other automobile touched the clothes of the woman and the rear mud guard of the defendant's automobile struck her and knocked her to the ground. The only evidence of the rate of speed was that he was going very fast. Guilty. 

Com. v. Sansone, 252 Mass. 71

The evidence offered was that the defendant drove his automobile in Adams Square at a rate of 30 miles an hour and collided with a woman. Guilty. 

Com. v. Peach, 239 Mass. 575

The defendant was tried on an indictment for manslaughter and on a complaint for operating so as to endanger. The judge charged that the negligence of the deceased has nothing to do with the conduct of the defendant. He also charged that if the jury found him guilty of manslaughter, he should be found not guilty of operating so as to endanger. If found not guilty of manslaugh-

CRIMINAL CASES 

ter they could find him guilty or not guilty of operating so as to endanger. Whether there could be a merger of the offenses was not considered. Negligence. 

Leonard v. Conquest, 274 Mass. 347

One is guilty of wanton, willful, and reckless conduct as well as guilty of an intended wrong when, being in the line of traffic with automobiles coming in the opposite direction and the road only wide enough for two lines of traffic, he pulled out of line at a speed of thirty miles per hour and continued to drive on towards the opposite line of traffic until he forced automobiles coming against him off the road. The jury could find that he rode with death and that no reasonable person would expect to be saved from great bodily harm. 

Butera v. Cuneo, 273 Mass. 181

When a drunken person attempts to operate an automobile, danger is always present, but if he acts as carefully as a sober man of ordinary prudence under the existing circumstances, he is not negligent. In this case automobiles were on the road going and coming, speed of 30 miles per hour. 

Cook v. Cole, 273 Mass. at 563

Discussion as to overloaded truck being a nuisance on the highway. 

Donovan v. Mutrie, 265 Mass. 472

The deceased started to cross the street and when one half way across started on a signal from an officer stationed there, deceased started to turn back and was killed. A driver even under the direction of an officer has no right to abandon all care for the safety of pedestrians relying on that expectation. 

Altman v. Aronson, 231 Mass. 588

Gross negligence is the omission of even such diligence as habitually inattentive and careless men do not fail to exercise in avoiding danger to their own person and property. It is an act of omission respecting legal duty of an aggravated character as distinguished from a mere failure to exercise ordinary care. 

Parker v. Moody, 274 Mass. 100 

Going 35 miles an hour on a slippery street, cutting in, and dodging in and out of traffic, disregarding a red light, and driving at top speed against traffic is negligence.

CRIMINAL CASES 

115 

Logan v. Reardon, 274 Mass. 83

Defective brakes, of which the defendant had knowledge, 45 miles per hour on a slippery road. Gross negligence.

Guinan v. Famous Players, 267 Mass. 516 

A violation of a statute, ordinance, or regulation, although not conclusive, is evidence of negligence on the part of the violator to all consequences that the statute, ordinance, or regulation is intended to prevent. 

Mason v. Thomas, 274 Mass. 60

An automobile being driven by an unlicensed driver who was accompanied by a licensed driver who was teaching him how to drive. The person injured was in the back seat. The unlicensed driver put his foot on the power instead of the brake, causing the automobile to tip over. Not negligence or gross negligence. 

Meeney v. Doyle, 276 Mass. 218

One who keeps lighting cigarettes and keeps turning around to see where the sparks are going, knowing the dangerous condition of the road, is guilty of gross negligence. 

Margeson v. Taxi Co., 266 Mass. 192

A pedestrian was crossing the street where there was a traffic tower. She did not know whether the lights were against her or not. She crossed the street and was hit by a taxi. Question of fact for the jury whether she was guilty of contributory negligence. 

Quinn v. Miller, 267 Mass. 84

Pedestrian crossing the street at a marked crossing. Automobile was approaching and swerved and hit the pedestrian. Negligence. 

Seymour v. Dunville, 265 Mass. 78

A runabout was approaching a truck; there were three persons in the seat of the runabout. When the runabout tried to pass the truck on the left-hand side of the road at a speed of 17 miles per hour for a distance of 100 feet abreast of the truck, the truck turned to the left and hit the runabout. Question for the jury as to the negligence, and the jury so found. 

Creplinski v. Severn, 269 Mass. 261

A woman who jumps from an automobile to escape the indecent conduct of the driver is not entitled to damages. She con-

CRIMINAL CASES 

tributed to the injury. It was not willful, reckless, or wanton misconduct. Speed. 

Davidson v. Taxi Co., 278 Mass. 540

One need not be an expert to testify as to speed, but in the discretion of the court one capable of judging and of sufficient knowledge and intelligence may testify as to speed. 

Com. v. Cassidy, 209 Mass. 24

It is a question of fact for the jury under all the circumstances, coming from all witnesses on both sides, with due consideration as to all conditions, having due regard to the traffic and the use of the way. 

Com. v. Crowinshield, 187 Mass. 221

A regulation that no person shall ride or drive in a specified avenue in a city at a rate of speed exceeding 8 miles per hour is reasonable. 

Com. v. Buxton, 205 Mass. 49

The use of speed recorder is admissible in the timing of automobiles. 

Banks v. Satran, 266 Mass. 253 

Twenty miles per hour around a corner on a snowy night is not gross negligence. 

Di Rienzo v. Goldfarb, 257 Mass. 272 

Going 20 miles per hour without sounding horn or giving a signal on a busy street is negligence. 

Cairney v. Cook, 266 Mass. 279 

A speed of from 5 to 7 miles per hour is not excessive speed of an automobile emerging from an alley. 

Boyd v. Mills, 278 Mass. 132

Five miles per hour on a level approaching a vehicle drawn by horses who showed no alarm is not negligence. 

Com. v. Bosworth, 257 Mass. 212

Twenty-five to thirty miles per hour, no accident, straight road upgrade, no pedestrians on the road, people on the sidewalk, one crossed thirty feet away, one car drove into the street at right angles, when the car of the defendant was two hundred feet away, cars on the side, parked at an angle, street fifty feet

CRIMINAL CASES 

117 wide between curbs, seven o'clock in the evening, was held to be unreasonable speed. 

Burke v. Cook, 246 Mass. 518 

A speed of 35 miles per hour, while prima facie evidence of a rate higher than is reasonable and proper if continued over a distance of one quarter of a mile, was not conclusive of negligence. 

Com. v. Arone, 265 Mass. 128 

Automobile going 15 miles per hour did not sound horn nor slacken speed. The windshield was dirty. The defendant was charged with manslaughter, was guilty of reckless and willful conduct in the operation of the car. He was in a position to see the person in the street. 

Wilcox v. Sides, 267 Mass. 70

A patrol wagon was going 25 miles per hour and was negligent in going within 8 feet of a car and hitting a person who had left the car and was approaching the sidewalk. The driver of the patrol wagon was violating Section 14 of Chapter 90 and was negligent. 

Going away after an accident and not giving name, etc. 

Com. v. Bleakney, 278 Mass. 198 "Knowingly collide" does not apply to the operator of a motor car against which, while it is stopped upon the highway, another person thoughtlessly or carelessly falls or walks. The position of the automobile is a mere condition and not the cause of the collision, and the operator is a passive participant and not the actor in the collision. 

Com. v. Nurmi, 250 Mass. 128

The defendant stopped and returned to the scene of the accident; he did not give his name, license number, or residence. 

Found guilty. He must conform to all of the requirements of the statute. 

Com. v. Massad, 242 Mass. 532

The pleader need not allege in the complaint the name of the person collided with, as it can be cured by a bill of particulars. 

Nager v. Reid, 240 Mass. 211

It applies only to the operator of the automobile and does not bind the owner who is not the operator.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Horsfall, 213 Mass. 232

If an operator goes away after an accident but, however, sends back a messenger to tell the facts that the law requires, even if the messenger fails to do this he cannot be convicted under the statute. The statute does not state whom should be notified, but the decision defines who shall. 

Com. v. Lewis, 286 Mass. 256

One Lewis collided with one Bauer who was driving an automobile. Lewis talked with Bauer and then went away in another automobile driven by a third person without making himself known in words, the facts required by law. A verbal disclosure of the name and residence must be made under the terms of the statute, and the fact that Bauer knew Lewis is not enough. 

There was no evidence that Bauer or any bystander knew the residence of Lewis, even if his name was known. 

Com. v. Saltman, 289 Mass. 554

The chauffeur collided with another car. The owner of the car which the chauffeur was in was in the seat beside him and was held by the court to be the man responsible. The court found that the owner retained the control of the operation of the automobile notwithstanding the fact that his chauffeur was the medium through which the control was exercised. 

Person responsible. 

Wheeler v. Darmochwat, 280 Mass. 553

The owner has the right to control the driver. If the driver is operating at a speed known to the owner to be dangerous and the owner does not interfere to prevent him, the owner may become criminally responsible. 

Com. v. Sherman, 191 Mass. 439

The owner may be convicted of operating an automobile. 

Even if he is not actually driving it, he is guilty of managing and controlling the operation of it. 

Using without authority.

O'Roak v. Lloyds Casualty Co., 285 Mass. 532 

One Mack allowed one McGinniss to take his car while he, Mack, was in a theater and to return the same at 11 P.M. He did not return it until the next morning, when he had had an ac-

CRIMINAL CASES 

119 cident. The court held that McGinniss had the auto with the express and implied consent of Mack and was the person responsible for the operation of the automobile. 

Police power. 

Com. v. Kingsbury, 199 Mass. 490

The legislature has the right to prescribe what automobiles shall pass over certain streets in cities and towns. 

Way and street. 

Denham v. County Comm'rs, 108 Mass. 202

A private way for the use of one or more inhabitants of a town laid out legally is subject to be used by the public. The person to whose house it leads to, however, is liable for the repairs, etc. 

Com. v. Wilkinson, 16 Pick. 175

A turnpike, a toll road, is a public highway. 

McCarthy v. Beckwith, 246 Mass. 409 

Com. v. Cassidy, 209 Mass. 24

An intersecting way is where streets intersect but do not cross; any way which joins another at an angle, whether it crosses or not. 

Com. v. Leone, 250 Mass. 512

A square formed by a confluence of five streets is a way. 

Fournier v. Zinn, 257 Mass. 575 "Intersecting way" applies whether the roads cross or not.

The point of intersection is a space common to streets where they cross each other, and not a point where the paths of automobiles would cross. The right of way applies whether they do or do not cross. 

Falk v. Carlton, 270 Mass. 213

Where a street is divided by a triangular grass plot the driver of an automobile need not take the right-hand drive necessarily. Section 14 of Chapter 90 does not apply. 

Com. v. Mara, 257 Mass. 198

The way was well lighted, over 36 feet wide between the curbs, houses on both sides, hydrants, car tracks, concrete paving. This is a way within the meaning of the statute.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Harris, 257 Mass. 434

There was no evidence of the width of the surface or the method of construction; no curb stones, paved surface, no lights or presence of car tracks; in general use for a long. Is not a way as defined in Chapter 90, General Laws. 

Com. v. Matthews, 122 Mass. 60

Evidence that the street had been used for 20 years, that the city had repaired it and lighted it, is admissible. It had been located and appropriated to the public use. 

Roberts v. Public Utilities, 262 Mass. 583

The commonwealth can prescribe conditions on which public ways may be used by automobiles. 

McDonald v. St. Com'rs., 268 Mass. 288

The board is clothed with power to lay out, relocate, widen, and discontinue highways, Acts of 1913, Chapter 536. No statute authorizes courts to revise the determination of the board, and in the absence of abuse or discretions, their decisions are not open to the re-examination by the courts. 

Com. v. Gammons, 23 Pick 201

The law of the road extends to all places appropriated either de jure or de facto to the passing with carriages, etc., whether they were so appropriated by public authority or general license of the owners thereof, express or implied; and such owners themselves, while using their land as a road, must conform to the law. This was a passage on a wharf. 

Com. v. Tucker, 2 Pick. 44

A public landing is not a way, and a town has no power to discontinue it. 

Com. v. Hubbard, 24 Pick. 98

Every town way is a highway for most purposes and may be described as such in a complaint. 

Com. v. Low, 3 Pick. 408 

Com. v. Newbury, 2 Pick. 51 

Exclusive interrupted use of a way for 20 years is evidence of a grant, but such way will be a private way and a nuisance on it is not indictable.

121 Constitutionality. 

Com. v. Newhall, 205 Mass. 344

The enactment of automobile laws superseded the traffic regulations and the power of the Street Commissioners to make rules and regulations. A traffic regulation conflicting with the statutory regulations would be nugatory. Review of the statutes on automobile operation in this state. 

Com. v. Giles, 217 Mass. 18

Automobile laws do not repeal the traffic laws and do not conflict with the law of the road. 

Park Commissioners. 

Com. v. Crowinshield, 187 Mass. 221

They may make speed laws and need not post signs announcing such speed regulations. 

Com. v. Tyler, 199 Mass. 490

The park commissioners have the right to make rules and regulations as to the operation of automobiles, and they are not affected by subsequent automobile laws. Definitions. 

Gifford v. Jennings, 190 Mass. 54 

Approaching: One coming up from behind another wagon is approaching under the provisions of Section 14 of Chapter 90 and must blow his horn to notify the wagon that he is approaching. 

Foster v. Curtis, 213 Mass. 79

Passing trolley: In passing a trolley car the driver of an automobile must drive to the left of the middle of the road, and this applies to automobiles passing electric cars. 

Salo v. A. A. Accident Co., 257 Mass. 303

Motor vehicle: The term motor vehicle includes both motor vehicles and motor cycles, and while every motor cycle is an automobile, not every motor vehicle is a motor cycle. 

Doherty v. Ayer, 197 Mass. 241

Automobile: An automobile is not a carriage under Chapter 84, Section 1, requiring cities to keep the highways safe for carriages.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Widtnak v. Lord, 269 Mass. 238

Trespasser: One who drives an automobile in violation of the law as to a revised ordinance or a traffic regulation is a trespasser on the highway. 

Pierce v. Hutchinson, 241 Mass. 557

Dealer: One who was a proprietor of a garage, dealt in cars, and had an agency is not a "dealer" engaged principally in buying, selling, and exchanging motor vehicles. Garage. 

Hayes v. Miskel Auto Co., 234 Mass. 198

The owner of a car is not required to enter the departure and return of his automobile on the books of the garage. 

Di Simone v. Barr, 254 Mass. 79

Under the registration of dealers they may loan cars for not more than five days but it must be successive days. License. 

Conroy v. Mather, 217 Mass. 91 

An unsigned license is invalid. One who does not have a license to drive and who is involved in an accident is not in the exercise of due care. 

Com. v. Saben, 275 Mass. 546 

A licensed motorist riding with an unlicensed operator does not have the burden of ascertaining whether the operator's purported license is in effect to avoid statutory penalty. 

Com. v. Magarosian, 261 Mass. 228

One having a license to drive a truck cannot operate a touring car. He is to be charged with operating without a license. 

Griffin v. Heustis, 234 Mass. 95

The owner of an automobile licensed as an operator is not authorized to operate the car for hire. 

Bourne v. Whitman, 209 Mass. 155

The fact that an operator of an automobile is not licensed does not make the automobile a trespasser. One whose license has expired and who is riding with a licensed person does not violate the law.

123 

Com. v. Theberge, 231 Mass. 386

An automobile, so called jitney, passing through a town and not stopping, does not take on or leave passengers; must, however, take out a license in that town. 

Com. v. Guillemonte, 243 Mass. 346

The fact that the deceased was not licensed to drive is not admissible in an indictment charging the defendant with driving his automobile recklessly. Violation of a criminal statute is evidence of negligence on the part of the violator, as to all consequences that the statute was intended to prevent. 

O'Hare v. Gloag, 221 Mass. 24

The defendant in an application for a license failed to disclose that he had an infirmity. His failure did not of itself render the license void, though it might be sufficient grounds for the revocation. Still, having been regularly issued, it is valid unless and until it had been revoked by the proper authority.

Although the operation of a registered automobile by a driver without a license and unaccompanied by a licensed operator is a crime, it does not render the operator a trespasser on the highway. The operator believed she had a license but the same had expired.

Weapon in the automobile. 

Com. v. Moscatiello, 257 Mass. 260

The defendant being the owner and driver of an automobile in which a revolver was found can be convicted of carrying the same. It was found concealed under the back seat. Publication.

Registration. 

Nonresident. 

Hanson v. Culton, 269 Mass. 471 

Jenkins v. North Shore Dye Co., 277 Mass. 440

Plaintiff lived in Rhode Island, worked in Boston from September 26 to April 27. Hired a room in Boston and stayed there three nights a week. Automobile was registered in Rhode Island.

The defendant had a regular place of abode in Boston, more than thirty days, though his domicile was in Rhode Island. He was a trespasser on the highway. 

Avilla v. Du Pont, 278 Mass. 83

One who has a domicile in Delaware and a summer home in Massachusetts for more than thirty days must register his automobile in Massachusetts. 

Gondek v. Cudahy Packing Co., 233 Mass. 105

A corporation incorporated in Maine, which for more than thirty days has had several places of business in Massachusetts, also one in New Hampshire, and has exclusive officers in Boston but none in New Hampshire does not come within the definition of "nonresident." Is a trespasser here. A nonresident as used in the automobile laws is "residents of the states or country who have no regular place of abode or business in this commonwealth for a period of more than thirty days in the calendar year." The defendant had a place of business in Massachusetts, therefore he was not a nonresident.

Illegal registration - Trespasser. 

Stevens v. City of Melrose, 205 Mass. 329 

Dudley v. Northampton Ry., 202 Mass. 443

One operating an unregistered automobile on the highway is a trespasser. See Acts 1915, Chapter 87.

A person who registers an automobile must not only pay but must display the register numbers thereon; paying alone does not satisfy the statute. An unregistered automobile is unlawful and creates a nuisance.

If an automobile was legally registered and did not display the number plates, it was illegally on the highway.

An automobile was registered as a pleasure car, later the body was removed and a truck body substituted and used for the delivery of goods. Held that it was illegally registered and was a trespasser on the highway.

If one partner drops from the firm and the new partner does not register the motor vehicle in the firm name, it is a trespasser on the highway.

A dealer loaned his plates to another person. In case of accident he is equally liable to the injured party, as he intentionally aided in the accident.

The name was correct, street address was correct, but the municipality was wrong. Illegally registered. 

Furtado v. Humphrey, 284 Mass. 570

A motor truck was not legally registered where it was owned by a partnership and a certificate of registration was issued in the trade name used by the partnership upon an application to which one of the partners had signed such name followed by his own name and which contained an affirmative answer to the question, "Is this vehicle owned by you individually?" 

Com. v. Henrich, 263 Mass. 579

a motor truck which was not registered in his name; defendant instructed that a number plate of his be put on this truck and used in his, the defendant's, business. Illegal registration. 

Wallace v. N. B. Ry., 259 Mass. 20

If the owner of a motor vehicle properly registered substitutes a new engine in the vehicle and neglects to have the same registered, he is an outlaw. See Acts of 1928, Chapter 187. 

Fairbanks v. Kemp, 226 Mass. 75

If, after a man applies for the registration of an automobile belonging to him, he dies and the commissioners, not being notified of his death, issue the certificate in his name, whereupon his widow, who is one of the executors of his will, places the register numbers on the car and operates the same on a public way, she is a trespasser.

A motor vehicle registered in the name of the "International Brotherhood of Steam Shovel and Dredge Men" as owner is not properly registered if it appears that it is owned in common by the members, over 300 in number, of a voluntary unincorporated association known as "Local No. 60."

Kilduff v. B. E. Ry., 247 Mass. 453

Where the legal title to an automobile was in the partnership and not in the individual member, the latter was not the owner for the purposes of registration. 

Crompton v. Williams, 216 Mass. 184

If one registers an automobile under the firm name, even if he is not a member of the firm, and the firm has not complied with the law as to filing the name with the City Clerk, the registration is legal. A, after he sold the business to a corporation, still retained the old registration. Legal registration.

Mussman v. Broderick, 271 Mass. 404
