Koley v. Williams, 265 Mass. 601 

An automobile was registered under the name of Mrs. John 

P. Williams. As a matter of law the legal name of the defendant, upon her marriage, became Ethel M. Williams. The wife takes her husband's surname, and the statute was complied with. 

Bacon v. B. E. Ry., 256 Mass. 30

If after marriage the wife causes an automobile to be registered in her maiden name, the automobile is illegally registered. 

Conditional contract of sale.

Temple v. Middlesex Ry., 241 Mass. 124 

An automobile can be registered under the name of either the vendor or the vendee. Where on a conditional contract of sale the title remains in the name of the vendor, the vendee can use it if licensed though not registered under the vendee's name. 

Hurnanen v. Ricksa, 228 Mass. 346

A motor vehicle held on a conditional contract of sale, the vendee is the owner inasmuch as he is the owner for the purpose of registering the same. 

Murray v. Inderski, 266 Mass. 220

If the seller retained the legal title to the automobile, registration in his name is authorized. 

Owner and part owner. 

Harlow v. Senman, 241 Mass. 462

Registration in the name of one part owner is valid as long as he operated or was in control of the car though the person driving it was the other part owner. 

Person v. Bara, 263 Mass. 502

One contributing to the purchase of an automobile and having an interest as an owner may register the same. 

Shurfelt v. McCartin, 235 Mass. 122

The law requires a registration in the name of any part owner who shall operate the vehicle by himself or his servants, and that the registration in the name of a nonoperating co-owner is an insufficient registration. This does not permit the lawful operation of that vehicle by any co-owner whose true name, residence, and address is not contained in the application for registration.

CRIMINAL CASES 

McTigue v. Ryan, 286 Mass. 515

One A bought an automobile. All papers were signed by A. 

It was registered in the name of B. B paid the insurance. A never made any payments. Used by A. Held that B was the owner; the contract for the purchase made in the name of A was in fact made by A as agent for B, an undisclosed principal. 

Kilduff v. B. E. Ry., 247 Mass. 453

Where the legal title of an automobile was in the partnership and not in the individual member the latter was not the owner for the purpose of registration.

Fine v. Kahn, 270 Mass. 557 

An automobile was registered under the name of Murray R. 

Fine. His true name was Morris R. Fine. At times he used the registered name. This was not an illegal registration. It is not such a use as called for by the statute. 

Stanley v. Wilbur, 258 Mass. 481 

A motor vehicle being operated by the owner on a highway under a registration that unknown to the owner contained a wrong number on the engine was a trespasser. This has since been changed by the Acts of 1928, Chapter 187. 

Com. v. Sherman, 191 Mass. 439

The fact that an automobile is registered by the defendant and in his own name warrants a finding that he was the general owner of it or that he had a special property therein, which gave him control thereof.

Trombley v. Stevens Duryea, 206 Mass. 516 

The certificate of registration in which the defendant's name appeared under a number corresponding to the number of the car was competent as tending to prove and establish by the identity of the numbers, either the defendant's ownership or the defendant's exclusive right to its exclusive possession and management. 

Nash v. Lang, 268 Mass. 407

A husband purchased an automobile with his own money and presented it to his wife. She made application for registration in her own name. In answer to the question did she purchase the automobile she answered in the affirmative. This inaccuracy in the statement in the application did not go to a

CRIMINAL CASES 

'129 vital matter, it did not affect the main purpose of registration, which is to afford identification of the owner and of the vehicle. 

Then again this question is not required by statute, although it is made on the general authority of the registrar. Properly registered. 

Fulton v. Stahl, 271 Mass. 23

One who in February sold a motor car properly registered in his name to a purchaser who then had the registration and the plates in his possession and who promised to send to the registrar the certificate with written notice of the transfer of the ownership, the name, place of residence, and address of the new owner but did not perform his promise was not liable in tort of personal injuries suffered in a collision with a vehicle two months later. while it is being driven by the purchaser and its registration still remained unchanged, where it appears that he was ignorant of the status of the registration. 

Dudley v. Northampton St. Ry., 202 Mass. 443 

A, a resident of Connecticut, who during sixteen days had been operating an automobile in Massachusetts except for part of a day when he drove it in Connecticut, and the daytime of another day when he drove into Vermont, and a day and a half when it was in a repair garage, has operated a motor vehicle in 

Massachusetts for a period exceeding fifteen days. His entry into this state and the running of the fifteen days was not interrupted by his excursions out of it. 

Chase v. N. Y. C. R. R., 208 Mass. 137 

Perhaps the word "sold" in the statute providing that, after registration in a certain way of an automobile or motor cycle by a manufacturer or dealer the automobile or motor cycle shall be regarded as registered until sold or let for hire or loaned for a period of more than five successive days should be held to mean "sold and delivered."

Downey v. Bay State Ry., 225 Mass. 281 

The word "owner" is not confined to persons in whom the legal title is vested and includes one in possession of a motor truck as its purchaser under an incompleted conditional sale by the terms of which the title is to remain in the seller until the purchase money is paid in full. It includes not only persons in whom the legal title is vested but failees, mortgagees in possession, and vendees under a conditional sale.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Tripp v. Shawmut Nat. Bank, 263 Mass. 505

The defendant acquired an automobile from a salesman of the Maguire Company and sold its salesman the car under a special conditional sale. The company afterwards sold the car to an innocent purchaser. Held that in the special conditional sale the ordinary rights of the vendee did not pass to the salesman; that the plaintiff acquired a title to the car which was superior to that of the defendant, and a decree was ordered that the plaintiff was the legal owner of the car. 

Shapiro v. Lyon, 254 Mass. 110

The operation of an automobile for a day without lawful registration plates and without a permit of the registrar authorizing the temporary use of plates is a violation of Section 6. No valid distinction in the lawful use of the highway is perceived between use without number plates and the use of unauthorized number plates. 

Peabody v. Campbell, 286 Mass. 295

A duly registered automobile registered in Rhode Island, which grants reciprocal relations with Massachusetts, can be lawfully operated in Massachusetts by a person not licensed in Massachusetts, but licensed in Rhode Island and who was not entitled to operate here without a license under the provisions of Section 10 of Chapter 90 as amended by 1922, Chapter 263, and he was not a trespasser. 

Brennan v. Schuster, 288 Mass. 311 

A student from New York driving a New York car registered in New York where he has a domicile, but who lived in a college dormitory from September 1929 to 1930 is not legally registered in this state and must take out Massachusetts registration. 

Topf v. Holland, 288 Mass. 552

A slight error in answering questions as to registration in the application does not invalidate the certificate if the identity of the applicant can be ascertained. 

Attaching number plates. 

Com. v. Henrich, 263 Mass. 579

The defendant told A to take a truck and deliver merchandise which was neither registered nor insured. The jury could find that the defendant had control even if he was not there, and he could be complained of for attaching number plates even if he did not operate the automobile.

CRIMINAL CASES 

131 

Parking. Registered owner is liable. 

Com. v. Ober, 286 Mass. 25

A registered owner of an automobile is responsible if it is parked in violation of the traffic regulations even if there is not any evidence as to who parked the vehicle or who took it away. 

The fact that it is registered in his name makes him responsible at all times. Guilty knowledge on the part of the registered owner is not essential. In a word, this is one of the unusual instances where a person at his peril must see to it that the rules and regulations are not violated by his act or by the act of another.

Pleading. 

Com. v. Clancy, 261 Mass. 345

In the complaint for using an automobile without authority the name of the way on which the same was being operated must be pleaded. 

Com. v. Gurney, 261 Mass. 309

In a complaint for operating so as to endanger the name of the way on which the automobile was being operated must be pleaded. 

Com. v. Mara, 257 Mass. 198

In a complaint for operating so as to endanger, etc., the pleader must aver the phrase "lives and safety," although the statute reads "lives or safety." 

Com. v. Massad, 242 Mass. 532

Complaint did not state the name of person collided with. 

Evidence as to who she was and that she was struck down is admissible.

AUTREFOIS ACQUIT AND CONVICT 

Burden of proof. 

Com. v. Wermouth, 174 Mass. 74

The burden of proof is on the defendant to prove the plea. 

Com. v. Chesley, 107 Mass. 223

The defendant must plead specially a plea in bar. Under a general issue the defendant cannot show a former acquittal.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Acquittal in the Federal Court. 

Com. v. Peters, 12 Met. 387 

Com. v. Ponzi, 256 Mass. 159.

A conviction in the Federal Court is not a bar to the same crime in the state court. In these cases there was a trial. 

When jeopardy begins. 

Com. v. McCormick, 130 Mass. 61 

Com. v. Murphy, 174 Mass. 369

When a jury has been sworn to try the issue and the trial has commenced, jeopardy then has begun and the district attorney cannot nol pros against the objection of the defendant. 

Want of probable cause. 

Com. v. Sullivan, 156 Mass. 487

The discharge of a defendant in the lower court for want of probable cause is not a bar to a subsequent indictment. 

By reason of variance. 

Com. v. Cabot, 241 Mass. 131 

Com. v. Weston, 241 Mass. 131

Not guilty by reason of variance is not a bar to a subsequent complaint. 

Dismissing the complaint. 

Com. v. Micheli, 258 Mass. 89 

Com. v. Bresant, 126 Mass. 246 

Com. v. Anderson, 220 Mass. 142 

Com. v. Roby, 12 Pick. at 501

The effect of dismissing a complaint without a trial is like that of quashing or entering a nol pros of the indictment, and a second complaint can be brought. In the Roby case there was a trial. 

Disagreement of the jury. 

Com. v. Purchase, 19 Mass. 520 

Com. v. Cody, 165 Mass. 133

Where the jury disagrees and is discharged the defendant can be retried and is barred from pleading autre acquit. 

Com. v. Campopiano, 254 Mass. 560

A defendant consenting to the discharge of the jury during his trial cannot thereafter assert jeopardy. An acquittal by

CRIMINAL CASES 

133 reason of variance is not a verdict on its merits, barring subsequent prosecution on the same evidence. 

Com. v. Galligan, 156 Mass. 270

Where a case is continued nisi the prosecution discontinued, it is not a bar to a subsequent indictment. 

What is involved in jeopardy? 

Com. v. Cabot, 241 Mass. 131 

Com, v. Campopiano, 254 Mass. 560 

Com. v. Roby, 12 Pick. 496 

Com. v. Bosworth, 113 Mass. 200

When a formal acquittal is pleaded in bar, two kinds of matters are involved, those of record consisting of the former proceedings and acquittal, and those of fact which involve the identity of the offense and of the person alleged to have been guilty. 

Verdict set aside. 

Com. v. Brown, 167 Mass. 144

The defendant on being convicted the verdict was set aside. 

The complaint was placed on file. He may be later indicted. 

The pendency of the indictment is no defense. 

Illegitimate children. 

Com. v. Michele, 258 Mass. at 91

An acquittal for the begetting a woman with child is a bar to a prosecution for neglecting to support it. 

Barnes v. Ryan, 174 Mass. 117

A discharge of a man after a hearing in a criminal court for bastardy is not a bar to a subsequent complaint against him in the same court for the same offense. This is like a dismissal for want of probable cause, but today the illegitimate child act supersedes the bastardy act. 

Bribery and larceny. 

Com. v. Crowley, 257 Mass. 590

Bribery and larceny are not the same even if convicted on the same evidence. It must appear that the offense charged in the two cases were the same both in law and in fact.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Exposing and keeping liquor. 

Com. v. Sullivan, 150 Mass. 314

Exposing and keeping liquor for sale is not a bar to a complaint for selling, even if the sale relied on at the trial of the latter was given in evidence at the former trial. 

Com. v. Lewandoski, 251 Mass. 550

An acquittal for exposing and keeping does not bar a conviction for a nuisance. The exposing was charged on a day certain, and a nuisance is a continuing one. 

Com. v. Chesley, 107 Mass. 223 

Com. v. Hogan, 97 Mass. 122 

Com. v. Lewandoski, 251 Mass. 550

A liquor nuisance is not a bar to a complaint for exposing and keeping. 

Com. v. Hudson, 14 Gray, 11

A conviction of a nuisance charge for selling liquor is a bar to a subsequent prosecution for a single sale within the period named in the nuisance, but the reverse is not so. 

Lewd and lascivious cohabitation and adultery. 

Morey v. Com., 108 Mass. 433

A conviction for lewd and lascivious cohabitation is not a bar to a subsequent for adultery, although proof of the same acts of unlawful intercourse is introduced at both trials. 

Receiving stolen goods. 

Com. v. Sutherland, 109 Mass. 342

An acquittal on a complaint for receiving stolen goods is not a bar to a prosecution of the same defendant upon another, without further proof of the identity of the offense and that the goods described in the second indictment are such that the averment of the first indictment might describe them. 

Nol pros. 

Com. v. Wheeler, 2 Mass. 172 

A nol pros is not a bar.

Nuisances. 

Com. v. Baldwin, 213 Mass. 238

An acquittal on a liquor case is not a bar to a complaint for a house of ill fame; both are nuisances but of a different character.

CRIMINAL CASES 

135 

Breaking and entering and larceny. 

Com. v. Hoffman, 121 Mass. 369

An acquittal on an indictment for breaking and entering a dwelling house and larceny of property of one Joseph Burnett is not a bar to a complaint for stealing in the same house at the same time the property of one Harry Burnett, without further proof that Joseph and Harry are the same person. Embezzlement. 

Com. v. Bosworth, 113 Mass. 200

The defendant was charged in the lower court with embezzlement and was acquitted. Later he was indicted in the Superior 

Court for embezzlement, the sum alleged in the indictment being for a greater sum than was alleged in the lower court. The motion in bar was sufficient, the district attorney having demurred to the plea he admitted its insufficiency. 

Assault and battery endangering life. 

Com. v. Cunningham, 13 Mass. 245 

Com. v. Goddard, 13 Mass. 455

An indictment charging an assault and battery endangering life is barred by an indictment charging assault and battery. 

Assault and battery and rape. 

Com. v. McAnn, 277 Mass. 199

A conviction of indecent assault and battery is not a bar to an indictment for assault and battery with intent to rape arising out of the same circumstances. 

Case withdrawn from the jury. 

Com. v. Cronin, 257 Mass. 535 

The case was before the jury. Remarks by attorney for the defendant caused the judge to withdraw the case from the jury. 

On a subsequent trial on the same facts a plea of former acquittal was overruled. 

Illegal nol pros. 

Com. v. Hart, 149 Mass. 7

On a lower court complaint the defendant pleaded not guilty and was put on trial and after the evidence had been heard against him the justice nol prossed the complaint and dis-

CRIMINAL CASES 

charged him. Held that this amounted to an acquittal, as it was against his consent, and the justice had no right to nol pros the complaint. 

As to different counts. 

Com. v. Leach, 160 Mass. 542

The defendant was convicted on the first count of a complaint and acquitted on the second and third counts. On a new trial being granted, the defendant filed a plea of former acquittal. The commonwealth abandoned the second and third counts, which cured it. 

As to plea bar. 

Com. v. Goddard, 13 Mass. 455

In a plea of former acquittal or former conviction it is not necessary to plead over to the offense, for if the plea be found against him he will be put to plead again to the indictment and the trial will proceed as if no previous proceedings had been made. 

Com. v. Farrell, 105 Mass. 189

After the defendant had been allowed to withdraw his plea of not guilty and file a plea in abatement for misnomer it was allowed. The indictment was quashed. This was not a bar to a subsequent indictment for the same offense, as he had not been put in jeopardy. 

Larceny and deemed to be larceny. 

Com. v. Tenney, 97 Mass. 50

If an indictment follows the words which make it a crime of larceny is "deemed to be larceny," this is not a bar to a later one which is pleaded as larceny.

Pendency of an indictment. 

Com. v. Drew, 3 Cush. 279

The pendency of an indictment is no grounds for a plea in abatement to another indictment in the same court for the same cause. 

Defective indictment. 

Com. v. Gould, 12 Gray, 171 

Com. v. Wade, 17 Pick. 395

Where the indictment is defective, the defendant is not put in jeopardy and a verdict would be nugatory.

CRIMINAL CASES 

137 

Concurrent jurisdiction. 

Com. v. Harris, 8 Gray, 470

A justice of a court having concurrent jurisdiction with the Superior Court, in a case where he has final jurisdiction finds the defendant guilty, and then holds him for the Superior Court. 

This is not a bar. 

Operating so as to endanger. 

Com. v. Jones, 288 Mass. 150

An acquittal of operating so as to endanger is not a bar to a subsequent indictment for manslaughter arising out of the same state of facts.

AUTOPSY 

Com. v. Jordan, 207 Mass. 265

The district attorney need not furnish a copy of the autopsy to the defendant. 

Money forfeited.

BAIL 

GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 276 

Reed v. Police Court, 172 Mass. 427

The defendant defaulted and the money was forfeited. Defendant was recaptured. This did not void the recognizance, the court had the right to forfeit it. Term construed to mean time. 

Recognizance cannot be construed as to require presence before the Superior Court from time to time and term to term until finally disposed of. See General Laws, Chapter 276, Section 81. 

Excessive bail, Whitney v. Putnam, 17 Mass. 175

If excessive bail is required, the court will, on a writ of habeas corpus, discharge the defendant upon his giving bail in a reasonable sum. 

Com. v. Hitchings, 5 Gray, 482 

Com. v. Whitney, 108 Mass. 5

The Constitution, Article 8, of the United States, prohibiting the requiring of excessive bail, does not apply to state governments.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Assignment of bail deposits. 

Way v. Day, 187 Mass. 476

A person making a deposit of bail money in order that another person arrested may be allowed to go at large retains the general ownership in the money subject to the pledge under the statute, and the prisoner after having been indicted and sentenced without making default, cannot make an assignment of the bail money which will be good against the depositor who owns it; however, the title to the money may be regarded as between the commonwealth and the prisoner.

Whether the equity can intervene, quaere. 

Whether the court can order the money deposited to be paid to any person other than the prisoner "or his order," quaere. 

Excuse for a default.

Way v. Wright, 5 Met. 380 

It is a good defense in a writ against bail that the principal, after they became his bail, was convicted of a crime and is in prison, and in such a case the surety need not bring the principal into court on a writ of habeas corpus to be surrendered. Overruling Parker v. Chandler, 8 Mass. 264. 

Surety retaking principal. 

Com. v. Brickett, 8 Pick. 138

A surety having bailed the principal in another state in the United States may take him in this state, and a dwelling house is not a castle for the principal, and if the door be not opened on demand the surety may break the door and take the principal from a bed. Whether this would apply if the parties came from a foreign nation was not determined. A surety cannot take a person from a military tribunal. Estreat. 

Com. v. Johnson, 3 Cush. 454

After a person has defaulted and the bail sued, it is then too late for the surety to surrender his principal as a matter of right, but the court in its discretion may allow him to do so. 

Com. v. McNeil, 19 Pick. 127

A recognizance to keep the peace may be estreated.

139 

Com. v. Braynard, 6 Pick. 112

If one gives a recognizance to keep the peace in a complaint for assault and battery and subsequently violates it, it can be sued on.

Surrender. 

Wheeler v. Wheeler, 7 Mass. 169

An administrator may surrender the principal for whom his estate went bail. 

Whitton v. Harding, 15 Mass. 535 

Way v. Wright, 5 Met. 380

If a surety surrenders his principal in court personally, it must be made a matter of record and in writing. 

Reed v. Maynard, 11 Allen, 394 

Surrendering by surety of his principal. 

Form of recognizance. 

Com. v. Downey, 9 Mass. 520 

Com. v. Daggett, 16 Mass. 447

A recognizance must cite the cause of the caption. 

Com. v. Merriam, 9 Allen, 371

The commonwealth can file an amended recognizance. 

Com. v. Field, 9 Allen, 581

A recognizance must be single. Two persons cannot be joined in a recognizance. 

Com. v. Merriam, 7 Allen, 356

The recognizance need not set out the whole offense. 

Crocker v. Black, 16 Mass. 447 

Must show in the recognizance the cause for taking it. 

Life of the recognizance. 

Com. v. Austin, 11 Gray, 330

A recognizance to abide the order and sentence of said court thereon, etc., until the final sentence, etc., applies after the Supreme Court has overruled and remitted the case for judgment.

CRIMINAL CASES Illegal recognizance. 

.Com. v. Baird, 9 Met. 407

A recognizance which the commissioner fails to file in the proper place cannot be sued on after the default of the principal. 

Com. v. Hatch, 5 Mass. 191

The court will not sustain a bond given to the commonwealth by one not authorized to bring suit. 

Com. v. Canada, 13 Pick. 486

A recognizance which acknowledges indebtedness to the commonwealth imposes no legal obligation unless it is taken in conformity to the law, and if a magistrate who admitted it had no authority, the recognizance is void. 

Com. v. Collins, 11 Gray, 465

A lower court which has no jurisdiction of a complaint, only possessing the power to bind over to the Superior Court, if they take jurisdiction and the defendant appeals the recognizance is void. 

Without bail. 

Dunlap v. Bartlett, 10 Gray, 282

The danger of death of the assaulted person in consequence of an assault is no ground for refusing bail on the charge of assault and battery only. 

Com. v. Trask, 15 Mass. 277

A defendant may be committed to the common jail without bail while the injured person is on the danger list in the hospital. 

One surety can be accepted when. 

Com. v. Bisch, 145 Mass. 375

Where a mittimus cites that the defendant must recognize with sureties, the magistrate can accept one surety. 

Professional bail. 

Com. v. Tsouporokakis, 267 Mass. 496 

Under the bail law, Chapter 276, Section 61 B, the word "occasion" means each separate defendant. There can be no joinder of defendants in a recognizance.

Married woman 

new surety. 

Com. v. Abbott, 168 Mass. 471

A married woman may enter into a recognizance. No defense to a suit against a recognizance that one similar had been entered in the lower court. The court has the right to order the defendant to furnish a new surety if it believes the old one is insufficient. 

Conditional on appeal. 

Com. v. N. Y. C. Ry., 206 Mass. 417

The giving of a recognizance by a person who is convicted before a district court is not made a condition of the right of appeal to the Superior Court or the allowance of the right to appeal. 

Com. v. Teevins, 143 Mass. 210

The defendant recognized in the lower court for her appearance before the Superior Court. In the latter court the offense is changed and the defendant defaults. Suit is brought against the surety. Held that the defendant recognizes to appear before the court on some complaint and is valid. 

Juvenile offender. 

Robinson v. Com., 242 Mass. 401

A juvenile offender may be admitted to bail the same as an adult.

BILL OF PARTICULARS GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 277, SEC. 40 

Com. v. Sinclair, 195 Mass. 100

The right of a defendant to a bill of particulars, where the indictment does not set out the charge fully, is an absolute one.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Ellis, 207 Mass. 572

The defendant's right to a bill of particulars is only when the charge is not fully, plainly, and substantially set forth. 

Com. v. Giles, 1 Gray, 466

Whether or not a bill should be given is exclusively within the discretion of the court. One indicted for being a common seller of liquor was furnished by the commonwealth with a list of names to whom sales were made. Evidence of other sales was inadmissible. It must be confined to the particulars specified. 

Com. v. Haywood, 105 Mass. 187 

Com. v. Davis, 11 Pick. 432

The bill of particulars is not a part of the record nor is the commonwealth limited to them at a subsequent trial of the case in the Superior Court. They affect the proof and mode of trial and not the indictment, and are subject to demurrer. 

Com. v. Althause, 207 Mass. 32

A bill of particulars is a narration of facts, and no conclusions of law are to be deduced therefrom. 

Com. v. King, 202 Mass. 379

In a complaint for larceny, the defendant is not entitled as a matter of right to a bill. It is discretionary with the court. 

Com. v. Drohan, 210 Mass. 445

The benefits of a bill are lost if defendant defaults.

Kneeland v. Emerton, 280 Mass. 371 

The blue sky law is constitutional.

BOARD OF POLICE 

Com. v. Plaistead, 148 Mass. 375

The Board of Police has the power conferred upon them by the legislature to make rules and regulations. A member of the 

Salvation Army playing a cornet without a license in a parade, creating no disturbance, is an itinerant musician.

BOARD OF HEALTH 

Com. v. Collins, 257 Mass. 580

The health department of the City of Boston is held to be under the control of the Health Commissioner, exercising the authority of the Board of Health. 

Com. v. Cohen, 250 Mass. 570 

When "dressed;" under General Laws, Chapter 94, Section 138, means any calf weighing less than 40 pounds when dressed, with head, feet, hide, and entrails removed, designates the time of weighing, the time when the calf is dressed. Entrails as used in the section means intestines, and does not include heart, liver, lungs, and windpipe, commonly called the pluck, nor the sweetbreads. The defendant cannot rely on the stamp of the inspector who stamped the veal at the time of the slaughter. 

Com. v. Drew, 208 Mass. 493

General Laws, Chapter 111, Sections 122 and 189, which gives the board of health the right to examine into all cases and source of filth nuisance, etc., does not give them the right to make regulations as to the sale of milk used and sold in a way not to affect the public health. 

Durgin v. Minot, 203 Mass. 226 

General Laws, Chapter 111, Sections 123, 124, 125, can order a nuisance abated, but that is not the method. 

Com. v. Wilson, 241 Mass. 406

Rot and spot eggs, so called, regulation is constitutionally sound. Form of complaint. 

City of Cambridge v. Trelegan, 181 Mass. 565

The board of health may or may not license the business of a slaughter house. 

Com. v. Prince, 203 Mass. 602

The state has no right to displace a local inspector. 

Com. v. Young, 135 Mass. 526 

Keeping 150 pigs is an employment, and if considered a nuisance must be proceeded against under the General Laws and not by a violation of a city or town regulations. The defendant was entitled to have notice of such nuisance served upon him.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Staples, 191 Mass. 384

The state board of health cannot delegate its powers to another body. 

Com. v. Feeney, 221 Mass. 323

The legislature can make laws relative to the taking of shellfish from contaminated waters. It is valid as an exercise of police power.

Cursing and contumeliously reproaching God is blasphemy.

BOND 

See under Bail. 

Com. v. Hearsay, 1 Mass. 137

Where there are two dates in a bond, one in the penal part and one in the condition, the penal part prevails. 

Surety Co. v. Nazzaro, 239 Mass. 341 Nazzaro v. Surety Co., 233 Mass. 74 

A bond is not the same as a recognizance in this commonwealth. In Connecticut they are synonymous. 

Ahrend v. Odoerne, 125 Mass. 50

If a party signs a bond as a party thereto, it is immaterial that he is not named in the bond. 

BOTTLES GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 110, SEC. 17 

Com. v. Brandon Farm Milk Co., 249 Mass. 531

The payment by a dealer or a customer of a sum in excess of the price of the milk constitutes a sale, and the purchaser can either retain the jar or return it. A prosecution under General 

Laws, Chapter 110, Section 22, will not lie.

Com. v. Andler, 247 Mass. 580

A complaint charging the defendant with having in his possession registered bottles without the consent of the owner does not set out any crime known to the law. He should have been charged under Section 18 of Chapter 110 for using registered bottles without any authority, etc.

BOUNDARY 

See under Jurisdiction. 

Com. v. Matthews, 167 Mass. 173

An offense committed within 100 rods of the line is a local offense and must be proved as laid. 

BREACHES OF THE PEACE 

Com. v. Gorman, 288 Mass. 294

A peace officer in the absence of the statute may arrest without a warrant for a misdemeanor which (1) involves a breach of the peace; (2) is committed in the presence and view of the officer; (3) is still continuing at the time of arrest and only interrupted so that the offense and the arrest form part of one transaction. Not every misdemeanor involves the breach of the peace, however. Possession of short lobsters is not a breach of the peace. Voluntary drunkenness in private is not a breach of the peace. Profane, obscene, and violent language used in a dwelling house to persons outside is a breach of the peace. An officer who sees a breach of the peace is likely to follow unless the offender is restrained may arrest without a warrant. A man carrying a gun and making threats and acting madly can be arrested, as this is a breach of the peace and may result in injury. 

Operating under the influence of intoxicating liquor is a breach of the peace and arrest may be made without a warrant. 

BREAKING AND ENTERING, DAY AND NIGHT TIME GEN. LAWS, Chap. 266 

With intent to commit a felony. 

Com. v. Doherty, 10 Cush. 52

In an indictment it need not be alleged that the defendant "then and there intended to commit the rape," nor need the

CRIMINAL CASES 

offense of rape be fully and technically set forth. "Did break and enter a dwelling house with intent to commit rape" is sufficient. 

Com. v. Newell, 7 Mass. 245

Breaking and entering a dwelling house in the night time to cut off an ear was not a felony, but now under General Laws, Chapter 265, Section 14, it is made one. 

Com. v. Reynolds, 122 Mass. 454

Upon an indictment for breaking and entering a building with intent to commit larceny, stating whether it was committed in the day or night time is immaterial, but the accused can only be punished for the lesser offense. 

Com. v. Williams, 2 Cush. 582

Breaking and entering and stealing in a building, the amount of the property stolen is immaterial. 

Different means of entering. 

Com. v. Stephenson, 8 Pick. 354

Lifting a latch and opening a door, though not bolted or locked, shoving up the window though not fastened, getting down a chimney are all breaking and entering. The offense consists in violating the common security. Cutting a netting made of twine in a window is a breaking and entering. 

Com. v. Strupney, 105 Mass. 588

An entrance to a dwelling house through a window or a door that was left partly open is not a forcible breaking and entering to constitute the crime of burglary. If the window be a little pushed up, it is not breaking and entering to obtain an entrance by lifting it higher. 

Com. v. Lowery, 158 Mass. 18

A, on the promise of purchasing, got the clerk to let him enter the store after bolting the door behind him. While the clerk was in the cellar A let B into the store, who secreted himself and subsequently robbed the store. To convict B it was not necessary for him to have touched the door; if he procured himself to be let in with a felonious intent, both were guilty of breaking and entering.

CRIMINAL CASES 

147 

Evidence of other breaks. 

Com. v. Wilson, 2 Cush. 590

Tools were found in the possession of the defendant; the ward of a key was made and fitted by the defendant to open the door of a building other than the one he was on trial for. Not admissible. 

Day time or night time. 

Com. v. Glover, 111 Mass. 395

The commonwealth need not show that the breaking and entering occurred on the same night, the breaking may be made on a Friday night and the entering before sunrise on Monday morning. 

Com. v. Lavary, 255 Mass. 327

If the commonwealth does not introduce evidence that the crime was committed between one hour after sunset and one hour before sunrise, the jury can render a verdict of day time. 

The defendant was indicted for night time. 

Com. v. Reynolds, 122 Mass. 454

If the indictment fails to allege that it was committed in the day or night time, the jury can only render a verdict for the lesser offense.

Pleading. 

Com. v. Grace, 265 Mass. 119

The value of the stolen property is immaterial in a prosecution on a complaint for breaking and entering in the night time. 

Com. v. Bowden, 14 Gray, 103

A certain building there situate, to wit, the shop of AB, is supported by proof that AB occupied only a room in the building of several rooms. 

Com. v. Thompson, 9 Gray, 108

In an indictment for breaking and entering, where a shop is occupied by two persons as tenants in common the shop may be described as the property of either. 

Com. v. Reynolds, 122 Mass. 454

An indictment for breaking and entering the dwelling house of A is sustained by proof of the breaking and entering a house owned by A but not occupied. A had constructive possession. It

CRIMINAL CASES 

is only for identification merely and not to give legal character to it. 

Com. v. McGonagle, 1 Mass. 517

Indictment for breaking and entering a store cannot be supported unless the allegation is that the store is in a building. 

Com. v. White, 6 Cush. 181

The passenger room of a railroad station having within it a separate enclosed room where books and tickets are sold is not an office.

Miscellaneous. 

Com. v. Trimmer, 1 Mass. 476

Feme Covert not chargeable for larceny jointly with husband. 

Removing a plank which is loose, and is not fixed to the freehold, in a partition wall of a building is not breaking within the statute. 

Com. v. Ruck, 20 Pick. 356

A count in an indictment alleging that the defendant broke and entered a shop with intent to commit larceny and did there commit larceny is not double. After conviction the district attorney can nol pros as to the breaking and entering, leaving the conviction to stand as to the larceny. 

Com. v. Hope, 22 Pick. 1

Upon a general verdict for breaking and entering a dwelling house in the day time with intent to steal and actually stealing therefrom, the defendant is to be sentenced for the house breaking and is not liable to a distinct sentence for larceny. 

Com. v. Perris, 108 Mass. 1

An indictment for breaking and entering a building with intent to commit a felony cannot be maintained without an averment of the ownership of the building. 

Joselyn v. Com., 6 Met. 236

In a complaint for breaking and entering a shop with intent to steal, it is not necessary to describe the goods intended to be stolen. A general intent to steal goods would complete the offense and therefore the averment of such intent without more is sufficient to charge it. If there were not any goods in the shop, the crime was complete by the breaking and entering with an intent to steal goods.

CRIMINAL CASES 

149 

Com. v. Moore, 130 Mass. 45

A charge of breaking and entering with the intent to steal the goods of one person is not supported by proof of breaking and entering with intent to steal the goods of another. 

BRIBERY GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 268, SEC. 8A, AND COMMON LAW 

Doyle v. Kirby, 184 Mass. 409

It is no crime on the part of the voter to receive a bribe or to sell a vote at an election. 

Com. v. Wooten, 201 Mass. 81

An offer to a deputy sealer of weights and measures is within the statute. 

Com. v. Lapham, 156 Mass. 480

A milk inspector, whether he was or was not an executive officer, was not decided Form of complaint. 

Com. v. Tsoffas, 250 Mass. 445

The examiner of the Registrar of Motor Vehicles is a public officer. 

Com. v. Crowley, 257 Mass. 590 

Bribery and larceny by false pretenses are distinct offenses. 

Com. v. Hogan, 249 Mass. 555

By solicitation or acceptance or both is the subject of one punishment. 

Com. v. Donovan, 170 Mass. 228 

Bribery of a municipal officer: form of complaint. 

Com. v. Murray, 135 Mass. 530 .

One who gives a public officer a bribe is guilty. If the money is out of the control of the defendant and a complete delivery is made, it is sufficient. It is not essential to the crime that the public officer be induced to accept it. If the public officer retains the money for the purposes of public justice, it does not change the aspects of the crime.

CRIMINAL CASES BURGLAR'S TOOLS, POSSESSION OF GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 266, SEC. 49 

Com. v. Johnson, 199 Mass. 55

In a prosecution for burglar's tools the photograph of the defendant cannot be introduced to the jury if there is any description of the defendant on the back thereof relative to previous crimes. 

Com. v. Conlin, 188 Mass. 282

Two defendants can be joined in one indictment. The tools were in the room to which the defendant had the key. It was constructive possession. 

Com. v. Anderson, 245 Mass. 177

If tools are in the possession of one who drove an automobile (in which the same were found), the defendant cannot be convicted even if it appears that both intended to use them in a joint undertaking. They were in the possession of the operator. 

Tools innocent in themselves are efficacious as a jimmy or a lock pick in the hands of a burglar. Nitroglycerine and dynamite are not tools, machines, or implements. Under the statutory form which is herein given a bill of particulars is not necessary. 

Com. v. Tivnon, 8 Gray, 375

It is not necessary that burglar's tools should have been originally made or intended for unlawful use. An indictment is maintained by proof of possession either actual or constructive. 

Guilty intent. By proof of possession by one defendant both intending to use them in a joint undertaking is not sufficient. 

Com. v. Day, 138 Mass. 186

Burglar's tools found on the defendant, although not included in the indictment, may be testified to. Evidence that the defendant had used the tools found in his possession in the commission of burglaries one ten days and one five months previous is admissible.

BUSINESS 

Com. v. Schwartz, 197 Mass. 107

A pawnbroker licensed to buy and sell secondhand articles gave a loan on a coat. He was complained of for conducting a

CRIMINAL CASES 

151 pawnbroker's business without a license. One act may be sufficient if it is the occupation and not the business of which he is complained. 

Com. v. White, 260 Mass. 300

The complaint charged "did engage in the business of transporting persons for hire, etc." The defendant, a cab driver, took a fare from Boston to Brookline and then took a fare from a point in Brookline to another point in Brookline. One single act is not engaging in business. Such act alone plainly was insufficient to warrant a finding that the defendant violated the rules and orders. It did not tend to prove that he was engaged in the business of transporting passengers from place to place in that town within the language of the rules. 

Goodowsky v. Rubenstein, 225 Mass. 448

The phrase "engaged in business" means at least that the business shall be carried on as a regular occupation or constant employment, as distinguished from a single isolated act. 

Com. v. Sorensky, 269 Mass. 460

A single resale of a ticket to a wrestling match warranted a finding that the defendant was engaged in the business of reselling tickets. This is distinguished from Com. v. White, in that the defendant Sorensky was walking up and down in front of the building offering tickets to divers persons. From the circumstances the court was of the opinion that he was doing business. The case of Com. v. White was only a single incident. 

BUCKETING 

Com. v. Pappathanous, 257 Mass. 459

The defendant received money for the purchase of stocks, never purchased them, and retained the money for himself. 

Could be made out by proof of embezzlement of obtaining money by false pretenses or of larceny at common law, or by a trick or fraud, such that no possession of the money had come to the defendant.

BUS LAW 

N. Y. N. H. & H. R. R. v. Deister, 253 Mass. 178

Constitutional. A bus line which operates between Boston and Brockton without a license can be enjoined as a nuisance.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Potter, 254 Mass. 271

One who operates a bus between Boston and Manchester, N. H., and stops at Lowell (not having any license under the bus law) is guilty. The fact that the defendant was operating a bus in part for interstate commerce afforded no protection. 

Roberto v. Pub. Utilities, 262 Mass. 583

The commission may revoke a license and certificate to operate after a hearing. It is a privilege, not a contract or property, and revocation deprived carrier of no vested rights. 

Com. v. Cate, 254 Mass. 248

A certificate and permit hold a condition precedent to the operation of motor vehicles for transportation for hire for passengers within the Commonwealth.

CHECK 

Bergman v. Granstein, 235 Mass. 378 

A post-dated check is not payment. 

Dana v. 3d Nat. Bk., 13 Allen, 445

Check drawn upon a bank for more than the amount of the drawer's funds on deposit creates no lien thereon, and gives the payee no right to the actual balance until the bank has agreed to pay pro tanto. 

Tremont Trust Co. v. Burack, 235 Mass. 398

The drawer of a check retains the right to countermand its payment at any time before it is paid or is certified to a bona fide holder for value. 

Dutton v. Bennett, 262 Mass. 39

In order to have the receipt of a check act as payment it must appear either expressly or implied that the check was accepted in payment. The mere sending of the check and the receipt of it by the defendant could not render it effectual as a payment. An immediate return of the check shows that it was not accepted as payment. 

Lowell Bank v. Sheridan, 284 Mass. at 601

Where the drawer and drawee and the payee are all in the same city or town a check to be presented within a reasonable

CRIMINAL CASES 

153 time should be presented some time before the close of the banking hours of the day after it is issued, and its circulation from hand to hand does not change the rule.

Cochrane v. Zahos, 286 Mass. 173 

Acceptance and retention by a creditor of a check from a debtor does not necessarily constitute a payment. Whether there is a payment depends upon whether the creditor accepts it as a payment, or so conducts himself that he was not at liberty to deny acceptance as a payment. This ordinarily is a question of fact rather than one of law.

Kelley Petitioner, 152 Mass. 432

After a child has been committed to the custody of the state board, the parent may show at any time during the period of commitment that he or she is a fit person and in a position to have the child returned.

Using membership in a club and using metal disks to pay for liquor is a subterfuge, and the club may be indicted as a nuisance.

Under General Laws, Chapter 140, Sections 181 and 182, and 

Chapter 265, Sections 1-16, it is a question for the jury whether the machinery of the club is a mere sham fraud and contrivance

CRIMINAL CASES 

to evade the law. The signing of a membership card, etc., does not take it out of the realm of public shows. 

COMMON AND NOTORIOUS THIEF GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 266, SEC. 40 

Rice v. Com., 12 Met. 246

Discussion and pleadings relative to a common and notorious thief. 

Com. v. Hope, 22 Pick. 1

Indictment was for breaking and entering and larceny. The larceny included in the general conviction of such combined offenses is in no case to be considered as one of three distinct larcenies rendering a party liable to be adjudged a common and notorious thief and consequently where a defendant was convicted at the same term of court of three such combined sentences from no one of which he could appeal, he was wrongly sentenced as a common and notorious thief. 

Haggerty v. Com., 3 Met. 457

If a person is convicted of three distinct larcenies at the same term of court, there must be a consolidated judgment against him, as a common and notorious thief; if separate judgments are awarded on the separate convictions, all these judgments are erroneous. 

Stevens v. Com. 4 Met. 360

A conviction on an indictment for larceny, which contains three counts, each describing different property and different owners, but each alleging the offense to have been committed on the same day, is not a conviction of three distinct larcenies and therefore does not require that the convict should be sentenced as a common and notorious thief. 

Com. v. McKnight, 289 Mass. at 547

Each larceny of which he was convicted was separate and distinct from the others. These larcenies were committed at different times. This statute and procedure are quite different from cases where a heavier sentence is imposed for a second conviction and where there must be an allegation of the previous conviction as an integal part of the offense.

CRIMINAL CASES 

155 

COMMON VICTUALER GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 140, SECS. 1-21. 

ACTS OF 1933, CHAP. 92 

Com. v. Graham, 176 Mass. 5

A common victualer is not a shop, and selling cigars on the 

Lord's day is a violation of law. 

Com. v. Mikel, 221 Mass. 70

One who is licensed as a common victualer cannot sell food to be taken from the premises. The food must be eaten on the premises. 

Com. v. Lavery, 188 Mass. 13

One carrying on the business under another name, not as an agent, even if he honestly believed he had the right, is guilty. 

There is no intent necessary. 

Friends v. Childs Co., 231 Mass. 65

Definition of a common victualer. Cases on seller's guarantee that the food was fit to eat. 

Deehan v. Johnson, 141 Mass. 23

Mandamus will not lie to compel the mayor of a town to sign a license, granted by the board of aldermen to the petitioner, to be a common victualer, if the mayor is not satisfied that the petitioner has so complied with all the provisions under the law as to entitle him to a license. 

City of Boston v. Epple, 221 Mass. 395 

All fees must be turned over to the city. 

McMinn v. City of Cambridge, 225 Mass. 104

An order voting a license of the sixth class to an apothecary is an order made by the city council as a licensing board and is no more subject to a veto by the mayor than is a similar order made by the licensing board itself in cities which vote for license. 

A mandatory writ shall issue compelling the mayor to sign. 

Mason v. Thompson, 9 Pick. 280

An innkeeper is chargeable for the loss of goods of his guest committed to his care unless the act is caused by an act of God, or of the common enemy, or by the neglect or fault of the guest.

CRIMINAL CASES 

If a person commits his horse to the innkeeper to be fed, he is a guest, although he does not himself lodge or receive any refreshment at the inn. 

Berkshire Woolen Co. v. Proctor, 7 Cush. 417

If an agent of a corporation engaged in their business, becomes the guest of an innkeeper and is robbed in the inn, while he is a guest, of money delivered to him by his principals, to be expended in their behalf, the innkeeper is liable therefor to the corporation. If a traveler puts up at an inn and is received there as a guest and makes an agreement with the innkeeper for the price of his board by the week, he does not thereby cease to be a guest and become a boarder. 

Burbank v. Chapin, 140 Mass. 123

An innholder is, in the absence of an express contract to the contrary, liable to a loss by theft of the property of his guest, although the guest knowingly fails to comply with a reasonable regulation of the inn, if the loss is not attributable to the noncompliance of such regulation. See 244 Mass. 340. 

Spring v. Hager, 145 Mass. 186

If a guest at an inn, retiring for the night, locks the door of his room, but omits to fasten it with a bolt, such omission, if the existence of the bolt is unknown to him, and his attention is not called to it by the innkeeper, is not such negligence as will preclude him from maintaining an action against the innkeeper for the value of his property stolen from him during the night. 

See 262 Mass. 41. 

Com. v. Wetherbee, 101 Mass. 214

One is guilty of presuming to be a common victualer although he does not keep the inn open at all times.

COMPLAINT GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 277, SEC. 15, ETC. 

GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 218, SEC. 32, ETC. 

Who may make a complaint? 

Com. v. Mullen, 176 Mass. 133

In renovated butter complaints the statute says that inspectors of milk shall make and institute complaints, but the law does not say that no one else can. 

Com. v. Carroll, 145 Mass. 403 

Anyone can make a complaint who is competent to make oath to it. 

Com. v. Murphy, 147 Mass. 577 

Com. v. McDonnell, 157 Mass. 407

A statute which reads "The Mayor and Aldermen shall prosecute to final judgment all violations," etc. is directory only and does not debar any other citizen from entering complaints therefor. 

Com. v. Rawson, 183 Mass. 491

In health regulations, city ordinances, and traffic regulations, any person can make a complaint. 

Com. v. Nott, 135 Mass. 269

An officer arrested the defendant, but someone else swore to the complaint who was not present at the commission of the crime. Any person could have sworn to it. 

What is a complaint? 

Com. v. Davis, 11 Pick. 432

The term complaint is a technical one descriptive of the proceedings before magistrates. A complaint may be made before one magistrate and the warrant returned before another one for a hearing; still in the latter case the hearing and proceedings would be rightly designated as a complaint in behalf of the Commonwealth v. John Doe. It applies as well to a complaint heard, considered, and tried as a complaint made before a particular magistrate.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Hobbs v. Hill, 157 Mass. 566

Under the drunkenness section the word complaint means the oral charge which may be made to a proper magistrate or court, and which is to be reduced to writing by the magistrate of the court. It sometimes means the complaint to which the defendant answers and sometimes the oral charge. 

Administering the oath. 

Com. v. Bennett, 7 Allen, 533 

The certificate of the magistrate "taken and sworn to" is sufficient. Sworn means sworn to. 

Com. v. Farrell, 8 Gray, 463

Where a complaint duly charging an offense is presented in writing to a magistrate and he administers the oath to the complainant and certifies it in the usual form, this is conclusive evidence of a compliance with the statute.

Must be set out with precision. See also under Pleading. 

Com. v. Phillips, 16 Pick. 211

A complaint must be set out with precision, directness, and fullness, so that the defendant may be prepared to meet it, and one that does not so state this is void. 

Com. v. McLoon, 5 Gray, 91

An indictment which states the year of the commission of the crime in figures only without prefixing the letters A.D. is insufficient. The date of the crime read the fifteenth day of July 1855. 

Com. v. Hutton, 5 Gray, 89 

Date of the crime, 3rd day of June inst. The date of the swearing, 4th day of June A.D. 1855. Insufficient. 

Com. v. Barnard, 6 Gray, 488

The crime must be charged as AB. of in the 

County of. and a complaint which does not so state is defective. 

Com. v. Freelove, 150 Mass. 66 

If a complaint omits the words "against the peace" it is not subject to a motion to quash.

CRIMINAL CASES 

159 

Com. v. O'Donnell, 150 Mass. 502

A complaint for assault and battery charged "against the peace of said Commonwealth" instead of "against the law, peace, and dignity of said Commonwealth." This is not fatal. 

Com. v. De Voe, 159 Mass. 101 

The complaint need not be under seal. 

Com. v. Sullivan, 14 Gray, 97

A complainant may sign by a mark without any attesting witnesses. 

Com. v. Barhight, 9 Gray, 113

A complainant signed a larceny complaint below the description of the goods and above the charge. It is not subscribed by the complainant as required by law. Must be subscribed in writing before being sworn to. 

Com. v. Keefe, 7 Gray, 332

The jurat of a complaint may be expressed in figures as "third of June A.D. 1856." A jurat is a mere certificate of the magistrate that the requisite oath has been taken. 

Com. v. Wallace, 14 Gray, 382

One signed S. W. Richardson and certified by the magistrate is good. 

Com. v. O'Brien, 152 Mass. 495

If a complaint purports to have been properly received and sworn to, and a warrant properly issued, oral evidence offered by the defendant to show that when the complaint was made and the warrant issued, the court was not in session, is incompetent. 

Com. v. Fay, 126 Mass. 235

If a justice of the peace issues a process, it must appear of record that he is commissioned as a trial justice, and the court cannot take judicial notice that he is not so authorized. 

Com. v. Flito, 210 Mass. 33 

Whether a complaint is void or not, not being under oath, the defendant was sentenced. Habeas corpus proceedings are not the remedy. Exceptions the only way. 

Com. v. Brown, 116 Mass. 339

Where an indictment duly charges the commission of an offense at a time before it is found, and the date of its present-

CRIMINAL CASES 

ment appears by the record of the court, an error in the date of the caption is immaterial. 

Com. v. Roark, 8 Cush. 210

The contents of a complaint and warrant lost after being returned into the court may be proved by secondary evidence and need not be in exact words. 

See also under Clerks of court.

Com. v. Pease, 16 Mass. 91

The accepting of a promissory note signed by the defendant as a consideration for not prosecuting is a compounding. 

CONFESSION 

Defendant need not be warned. 

Com. v. Soaris, 275 Mass. 291 

Com. v. Seczepanek, 235 Mass. 411 

Com. v. Callahan, 108 Mass. 421

A confession otherwise relevant does not become irrelevant because the defendant was not warned. 

What is a confession? 

Com. v. Gleason, 262 Mass. 185 A confession is an acknowledgment of guilt. An admission is a statement by the accused tending to prove his guilt. 

Com. v. Pappas, 243 Mass. 519

As to a confession differing from an admission, see discussion under title of admissions. 

When admissible. 

Com. v. Killion, 194 Mass. 153

A confession to be admissible must be unsolicited and corroborated. 

Com. v. Chabback, 1 Mass. 143

A confession of guilt obtained by a promise of favor is inadmissible.

CRIMINAL CASES 

161 

Com. v. Reagan, 175 Mass. 335

When offered, it is a question for the court whether it was voluntary or not; if it is conflicting it then becomes a question for the jury. 

Com. v. Storti, 177 Mass. 339

As to a voluntary confession when the defendant cannot speak English. 

Com. v. Drake, 15 Mass. 161

A confession voluntary made to a clergyman or a priest for the sake of easing his conscience is admissible. 

Com. v. Wesley, 166 Mass. 248

The fact that the confession was signed and sworn to did not render it inadmissible. 

Com. v. Curtis, 97 Mass. 574

An officer said to a prisoner, "As a general thing it is better for a man who is guilty to plead guilty, for he gets a lighter sentence." Is not admissible. 

Com. v. Howe, 9 Gray, 110

A confession by one so intoxicated as not to understand is not evidence of guilt, and the defendant may introduce evidence to disprove the facts he is said to have confessed to. 

Whether he was so much intoxicated is for the jury. 

Com. v. Culver, 126 Mass. 464

The commonwealth offers the confession of the defendant. 

Defendant objects on the ground that it was obtained by the offers of favor. Commonwealth denies this. The defendant offers evidence to prove that the commonwealth did this; it is admitting the confession. 

Com. v. Corcoran, 182 Mass. 465

The mere fact that the defendant was under arrest when the confession was made is no grounds for excluding it. A gift made after a confession is no grounds for exclusion. 

Com. v. Sego, 125 Mass. 210

After the arrest of a clerk his employer said to him, "I am satisfied that there are others in this with you. I should like you to make a clean breast of it." This did not contain a promise or a threat and is admissible.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Sherman, 234 Mass. 7

One made six days after the commission of the crime is admissible; also to the conversation regarding a crime five months earlier. 

Com. v. Flood, 152 Mass. 529

A doctor when called to attend a woman found her to be pregnant. The defendant on being questioned by the doctor admitted the paternity of the child. Later in the doctor's office the defendant asked the doctor to get rid of the child. He again admitted the paternity and also having intercourse with the woman. Three witnesses who were in hiding overheard the confession. It was admissible, not having been obtained unlawfully. 

Com. v. Stevens, 10 Mass. 181

One indicted for altering a written order payable to the defendant. The defendant confessed that another did it, he, the defendant, knowing and assenting to it. Admissible. 

Com. v. Waite, 5 Mass. 260

The confession of a witness in a criminal prosecution as to his incompetency cannot be admitted to disqualify him. 

Confession in another tribunal. 

Com. v. Bradford, 126 Mass. 42

Defendant on trial for burning a building. The voluntary confession of the defendant before a fire inquest, reduced to writing and signed by him, is admissible. 

Accomplices. See under title of Accomplices. 

Com. v. Call, 21 Pick. 515

The confession of an accomplice made in the presence of the defendant and assented to by him is admissible in evidence against him. 

Com. v. Knapp, 10 Pick. 477

If an accomplice receives a promise from the district attorney that he will not be tried if he turns state evidence and he makes a full disclosure, and upon such promise he makes a confession, but afterwards refuses to testify, he may be put on trial and this same confession will be admissible against him. 

Com. v. Ackert, 133 Mass. 402

At a trial an accomplice testified to the guilt of herself and another defendant, and on cross examination testified that a

CRIMINAL CASES 

163 confession made to the officer who arrested her was given in exchange of a promise to have the protection of the law and favors extended by the officer. Held that the commonwealth might show that the confession was voluntary.

Two defendants, one confesses. 

Com. v. James, 99 Mass. 438 

Where two persons are jointly indicted for murder and there was evidence tending to show that one of them had made a confession which implicated both, it was held that separate trials should have been granted. 

As to other crimes. 

Com. v. Morrissey, 175 Mass. 264

The defendant testified in a bastardy case of having connection with the woman. This confession used in a complaint for adultery need not be corroborated, the indisputed facts being sufficient. 

Com. v. Tarr, 4 Allen, 315

A confession by one that he had committed adultery is admissible even if uncorroborated, and taken with other facts of an opportunity to commit the crime will warrant a conviction. 

Com. v. Howe, 2 Allen, 153

If a confession is introduced to prove that the defendant was in possession of a part of the stolen property and gave it to his mother, it is competent for him to prove in his defense that his mother never had the property in question from him. 

CONSPIRACY 

Definition.

GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 218, SEC. 26 

Com. v. Warren, 6 Mass. 74

The gist of the offense is to cheat, and if that be shown, it is not necessary to show any act done in consequence of it. 

Com. v. Davis, 9 Mass. 415

In an indictment for conspiracy the conspiracy is the gist of the offense and the cheating is but an aggravation.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Atty. Gen. v. Tufts, 239 Mass. 458

A conspiracy may exist even though the purposes of the participants or some of them may not be discovered by the means open in the trial of causes. It is not essential to a conspiracy that the parties met or conferred and formulated their plans; the common purpose may be inferred from a concerted action converging to a definite end. While a conspiracy must be a combination of two or more persons by some concerted action to accomplish some purpose criminal and unlawful, or to accomplish some purpose not in itself criminal or unlawful by criminal or unlawful means, it is not necessary that all of the conspirators join in every part of the unlawful transaction. 

Jurisdiction. 

Com. v. Saul, 260 Mass. 97

A prosecution for conspiracy may be maintained in the district where the illegal design originated or where the overt act was committed. One participating in a conspiracy planned outside the commonwealth whereby the owner of goods was procured by another conspirator to deliver the goods to a common carrier in Boston for transportation to a company in Philadelphia controlled by the conspirators may be indicted and convicted of a conspiracy to steal although he was not in the commonwealth at any time during the carrying out of the conspiracy. 

Merger of offenses. 

Com. v. Kingsbury, 5 Mass. 106

Where a felony or misdemeanor is in fact committed, a conspiracy to commit such a felony cannot be punished as a separate offense. 

Archibald 2, page 1048 note

A conspiracy to commit a misdemeanor is not merged in the misdemeanor, the result of the conspiracy when committed, but when the crime perpetrated is of the higher grade of offense than a misdemeanor, the misdemeanor is merged in the crime. 

2 Whartons Precedents, 907 

Com. v. Marsino, 252 Mass. 225

Where crimes are of an equal grade there cannot be a technical merger, hence, since conspiracy is only a misdemeanor, there can be no merger when the objective of the conspiracy is ac-

CRIMINAL CASES 

165 complished, if that objective be only a misdemeanor. But if that object is a felony, the conspiracy is merged upon the accomplishment of the felony. Pleading. 

Com. v. Barnes, 132 Mass. 242

In an indictment for conspiracy the pleader must allege an unlawful purpose. 

Com. v. Dyer, 243 Mass. 472

All the conspirators need not be named in the indictment. 

Com. v. Harris, 232 Mass. 588

A conspiracy to receive stolen goods must be pleaded continuando. A conspiracy to do an unlawful act is complete when the conspiracy is made and any act done in pursuance of it. 

Com. v. Fuller, 132 Mass. 563

An indictment for conspiracy need not allege any actual injury to the person intended to be defrauded, or any denial of the truth of the various false pretenses. It must be set out the intention to cheat some certain person, the conspiracy to do so, the property out of what he was to be cheated, and the means by which the conspiracy was to be accomplished. 

Com. v. Walker, 108 Mass. 309 

A conspiracy to obtain by false pretenses goods on credit need not allege a complete description of the goods. To obtain them generally is within the statute. Form of indictment. 

Com. v. Shedd, 7 Cush. 514 

Must aver the means intended to be used. 

Com. v. McCarthy, 272 Mass. 107 

A complaint alleged a conspiracy on the 15th day of August.

The bill of particulars stated that the conspiracy was formed on or about that date, evidence that in the latter part of June or the first of July was admitted. The commonwealth is not bound by the date. Form for committing a conspiracy in the future. 

Com. v. Wallace, 16 Gray, 221 

If the means are by cheat, false pretenses, or fraud, the facts must be set forth. Present form for.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Eastman, 1 Cush. 189

When a conspiracy is plainly and technically set forth, the acts done in pursuance of it need not be set forth, or if set forth need not be proved. 

Com. v. Tibbetts, 2 Mass. 536

In a conspiracy to accuse one of a crime it is not necessary to allege that the defendant procured or intended to procure an indictment or other legal process. 

Com. v. Stuart, 207 Mass. 563

The indictment alleges one date. Evidence can be introduced covering five years, including preparations for the conspiracy made by the chief conspirator. 

Com. v. Judd, 2. Mass. 329

An indictment charging a conspiracy to sell and manufacture is indictable although no sale is made. 

Com. v. Downey, 288 Mass. 147

Did conspire together to commit thereafter from time to time and on different occasions as opportunity should offer, and not at any time then particularly set and fixed, the crime of conspiracy, requesting one AB a gift or gratuity with the understanding that C and D, police officers, would abstain from performing an act within the course of duty as police officers (conspiracy to solicit bribes). If the indictment is insufficient, the defendant has recourse to Chapter 277, Section 34, of the General Laws. 

Com. v. Harris, 232 Mass. 588 

The charging of a conspiracy continuando is good. 

Com. v. Riseman, 257 Mass. 254

A misdemeanor and a felony could be joined in one indictment. 

Com. v. Dyer, 243 Mass. 472

In an indictment for conspiracy a general description of the illegal means employed by the defendant by terms of recognized meaning in law is sufficient, without the particularity which might be necessary in an indictment for a substantive crime. 

It is not open to the objections that it is vague.

167 

Com. v. McCarthy, 272 Mass. 107 

Com. v. Coshnear, 289 Mass. 516 at 522

They were charged in an indictment for conspiracy to steal property of persons unknown.

Evidence. 

Com. v. White, 208 Mass. 202

The admission by the defendant in the case is only to be used against himself, but in an indictment for bribery it can be introduced as against all defendants. 

Com. v. Perry, 248 Mass. 19

The testimony of a co-conspirator to conversations with an officer after the commission of the crime alleged in the indictment is inadmissible, he the co-conspirator having pleaded guilty. 

Com. v. Smith, 163 Mass. 411

The defendant who takes the witness stand cannot avail himself of his constitutional rights regarding questions tending to incriminate him, as can an ordinary witness who is not a party. 

Com. v. McKenzie, 211 Mass. 578

One defendant may be tried even if the other defendant is not arrested. Statements made by the absent defendant may be used after a case has been established at the discretion of the court. Statements made by one of the defendants in the grand jury room are admissible. 

Pickett v. Walsh, 192 Mass. 572 

What is unlawful for an individual may be unlawful when done by a combination of individuals. 

Com. v. Pelletier, 240 Mass. 264 

Com. v. Corcoran, 252 Mass. 465

Where there is evidence of a conspiracy the acts and declarations of each conspirator in pursuance of a common design, whether in the presence or absence of the fellow conspirators, is admissible against all. 

Com. v. Meserve, 154 Mass. 64

It is not a variance if some of the contemplated means are not proved as alleged, provided any criminal or unlawful means which are alleged are proved.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Lane, 254 Mass. 46

Acts of conspirators in carrying out a common scheme were the acts of all the conspirators and confederates, and a verdict of guilty was warranted. The defendant, a teller in a bank, juggled checks and placed the money stolen with book makers; they knew his connection with the bank and that the money he paid them was the money stolen from the bank. 

Com. v. McDermott, 255 Mass. 575

Declarations made by one conspirator in the course of the conspiracy before it has come to an end are admissible against the other conspirators. Declarations made after the conspiracy has ended bind only the maker and cannot be used in evidence against the other members of the conspiracy not present when they were made. 

Com. v. Devereaux, 256 Mass. 387

If a robbery had begun and was taking place at the time a murder was committed, it is murder in the first degree; if the murder took place after the robbery was committed, it would not so be. Two defendants, after committing the robbery, sat in an automobile waiting for the third defendant, who killed a person while committing the robbery. The robbery was not over, they were still in the possession of the stolen money, and the plan was not completed. 

Willett v. Herrick, 242 Mass. 471

A conspiracy may be unlawful by reason of its purpose or by reason of the means employed to accomplish its purpose. 

Cooke v. Greene, 180 Mass. 525

It is no duty to prevent the commission of a crime which the defendant knows is about to be committed. 

Com. v. Cohen, 127 Mass. 282

Conspiracy with A to commit a crime; the defendant mingled with A to get A to commit a crime; he was an officer and led the defendant on to commit the crime. A is guilty. 

Fox v. Com., 264 Mass. 51 

First count, conspiracy to sell liquor. Second count, selling liquor. Third count, liquor nuisance. The defendant can be sentenced on all three counts. It is not in error to sentence the defendant for conspiracy to do the very same thing for which

CRIMINAL CASES 

169 he was sentenced under the indictment against him as an individual. The statutory misdemeanors were distinct offenses from the crime of conspiracy which in this commonwealth is a misdemeanor at common law. The remedy where the law permits the punishment for conspiracy to commit crimes to exceed the penalty for the crimes is with the legislature. 

Com. v. Prius, 9 Gray, 127 

To overinsure property is not a crime. 

Com. v. Waterman, 122 Mass. 43

Evidence of a conversation with one of the defendants involving by way of admission the guilt of the others is competent if aside therefrom there is sufficient evidence to justify the inference that they were parties to the conspiracy. 

O'Brien v. McNulty, 122 Mass. 474

The commonwealth must first show evidence of a conspiracy before evidence of the conspirators can be introduced. 

Com. v. Farese, 265 Mass. 377

It is not essential to a conspiracy that the parties meet or that they confer and formulate their plans. A common purpose may be inferred from concerted action converging to a common end. 

Com. v. Kellogg, 7 Cush. 473

To defraud an insurance company. Indictment charged removing goods, and then on learning that goods were insured, a demand made on the insurance company. This evidence will not support an indictment. 

Com. v. Hunt, 4 Met. 111

To accomplish some purpose not in itself criminal or unlawful by criminal or unlawful means. An association the object of which is to adopt measures that have a tendency to impoverish a person, to diminish his gains or profits is unlawful. Fully discussed by Shaw, C. J. 

Com. v. Harley, 7 Met. 506

An averment in an indictment that the defendants conspired to defraud Smith is not supported by proof that they conspired to defraud the public generally or any individual whom they might meet and be able to defraud.

CRIMINAL CASES Livermore v. Herschell, 3 Pick. 33 

An indictment against three defendants. One made representations in the absence of the other two defendants in pursuance of a previous agreement; will sustain an indictment against all three. 

Com. v. Crowinshield, 10 Pick. 497

To make the declarations and actions of a conspirator in the furtherance of the common object admissible in evidence against a co-conspirator, it is sufficient that the conspiracy has been proved by a competent witness. The court will decide on his credibility. 

Com. v. Marsino, 252 Mass. 224

Stealing does not become merged in the crime of larceny when the conspiracy is successful to such an extent that those committing the crime cannot be convicted upon indictments for both offenses. Stealing from a National Bank was not merged in the committed felony.

Davis Criminal Justice, 393 

Archibald 2, page 1048 note

It is sufficient to allege the intent to defraud, without naming the particular person to be defrauded, but if the complaint avers that the defendant conspired to defraud A, the proof must correspond with the allegation. It is not necessary to allege overt acts in the indictment. In an indictment for conspiracy to effect an object which is criminal in itself, the means intended to be used need not be set out. In an indictment for a conspiracy to do an act which is an offense merely by statute, the means intended must be set out. 

Com. v. Coshnear, 289 Mass. 516

At the trial for conspiracy it is proper to permit the jury, provided they find the conspiracy proved, to consider as against each and all conspirators the acts and declarations by one of them in pursuance of the common object while the conspiracy is pending.

CONSTITUTIONAL LAW 

See under Statutes. 

Com. v. Hana, 195 Mass. 262

When a case involves the punishment of a crime, the constitutionality of the statute may be questioned at any time in the proceeding.
