171 

Com. v. Wilkins, 243 Mass. 356

Under Article 4 of the Constitution, Federal decisions are not binding on the courts of this state in construing Article 14 

of the Massachusetts Declaration of Rights. 

Com. v. Rice, 261 Mass. 340

The Police Commissioner has the right to license certain places for taxicabs to the exclusion of all others. The granting of a certain part of a street is not an unconstitutional exercise of police power. 

Wyeth v. Bd. of Health, 200 Mass. 474

A board of health regulation requiring that no permit shall be issued unless the applicant is an embalmer is unconstitutional. 

Opinion of Justices, 208 Mass. 619

A state cannot make laws telling the citizens that they cannot work more than 8 hours a day, but it can make laws providing that it and any town or city which has accepted the provisions of this act can make contracts prohibiting citizens from working more than 8 hours a day. 

Com. v. Roy, 140 Mass. 432

A city ordinance which goes outside the scope of what the statutes provided for is void. Punishment.

CONTEMPT 

Brewer v. Casey, 196 Mass. 384

Punishment for contempt must be in the common jail. 

Hurley v. Com., 188 Mass. 443

Under General Laws, Chapter 220, Section 14, the punish ment must be in the common jail, and the provision that persons committed to the jail can be served in the House of Correction does not apply. Can be reviewed by the Supreme Court on a writ of error. Appeal. 

N. Y. C. v. Ayer, 253 Mass. 122

Whether an appeal lies is an open question. It has been said to be open to grave doubt whether exceptions lie in proceedings for direct contempt of court.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. McNary, 246 Mass. 46

The ultimate decision must be against the defendant following the above case. 

When the power of court is lost. 

Clark's Case, 12 Cush. 320

The power of a magistrate to punish for contempt is incidental to the power invested in him relative to the pending proceedings, and cannot be exercised by him after that proceeding is finished, or at least for the term in which it is finished. 

Who may commit. 

Hurley v. Com., 188 Mass. 443

If committed in the presence of the court, no complaint is necessary. If otherwise, a formal presentation to the court signed by a sworn prosecuting officer is a verification of the facts constituting a constructive criminal contempt to justify criminal action. 

Blankenburg v. Com., 272 Mass. 25

A warrant for arrest for contempt is not necessary in case of a contempt committed in the presence of the court. 

Lawson v. Rowley, 185 Mass. 171

A justice of the peace has no power to commit for contempt. 

His remedy is under Section 11 of Chapter 233 of the General Laws. 

Whitcomb's Case, 120 Mass. 118

The common council of a city has no power to commit and punish for contempt. It is not a legislature. It has no power to make laws but merely to pass ordinances upon such local matters as the legislature may commit to its charge. It has no judicial functions. 

What is contempt? 

Com. v. McNary, 246 Mass. 46 

Sending a letter to the Grand Jury is contempt. 

Com. v. Perkins, 225 Mass. 80

Obstructing justice. It is a crime for a person to agree to obstruct justice by procuring a witness to absent himself from trial, although he is not under process to attend.

CRIMINAL CASES 

173 

Globe Newspaper Co. v. Com., 188 Mass. 449

For the analysis for contempt when published by a newspaper. 

Telegram v. Com., 172 Mass. 294

A corporation may be indicted for contempt and a fine collected under a writ. 

No jury trial. 

Walton Lunch Co. v. Kearney, 236 Mass. 310

Not entitled to a trial by jury. Discussion of the power of the courts to grant an injunction. 

N. Y. C. v. Ayer, 253 Mass. 122

A contempt proceeding is not strictly civil or criminal. The amount of the fine is not open to revision. 

Hurley v. Com., 188 Mass. 443

The Superior Court has the right to review proceedings on a writ of error. 

Liability of committing justice who errs. 

Piper v. Peterson, 2 Gray, 120

A justice who, having no jurisdiction in a case, commits a person for contempt is liable in an action by the witness. 

CONVICTION 

Fay v. Harlan, 128 Mass. 244

The word "conviction" used in a sense of using a record to impeach the credibility of a witness is used in the sense including judgment of the court, and a plea of guilty without such final judgment is not sufficient. 

Com. v. Chiovaro, 129 Mass. 489 

Com. v. Lockwood, 109 Mass. 323

The conviction of a crime is never complete until judgment has been rendered upon that conviction. 

Atty. Gen. v. Pelletier, 240 Mass. 264 "Offenses" as used in the constitution is the equivalent of "crime." "Conviction," as used in the General Laws, Chapter 233,

Section 21, implies judgment and sentence of the court upon the verdict, or confession of guilt; a mere verdict of guilty is not

CRIMINAL CASES 

enough. Nothing less than a final judgment conclusively establishing guilt will satisfy the meaning of the word "conviction." 

See 214 Mass. 343, 99 Mass. 422, 187 Mass. 288, 150 Mass. 326, 170 Mass. 362. 

Com. v. Kiley, 150 Mass. 325 

Com. v. Gorham, 99 Mass. 420

The word "conviction" means a final judgment. The voiding of a license on a finding of guilty, the case being appealed, is not constitutional. 

Green v. Com., 12 Allen, at 165

The judgment of conviction is included in the sentence, that being the final act in a criminal proceeding, which can follow only on a due conviction, and constitutes the judgment that the party is guilty of the crime charged. In England the form of making up the record includes a formal adjudication of guilt, but this form has not been adopted in this state. 

Marino v. Hibbard, 243 Mass. 90

Conviction ordinarily means a conclusive establishment of guilt, and imports that the question of guilt has been adjudicated, and is not open to further inquiry as of right by the person convicted. 

Com. v. Baldi, 250 Mass. 528 

Com. v. Richards, 17 Pick. 296

Conviction under Section 30 of Chapter 278 of the General 

Laws may include the judgment of the court following a verdict of guilty or a confession of guilt, or may mean a verdict of guilty against the defendant, or his confession in open court without judgment or sentence.

CORPORATIONS 

Lower courts have jurisdiction. 

Com. v. N. Y. C. R. R., 206 Mass. 417

All lower courts have jurisdiction in complaints against corporations. Intent. 

Com. v. Prop. New Bedford Bridge, 2 Gray at 345 

Telegram v. Gazette, 172 Mass. 294

Corporations cannot be indicted for offenses which derive their criminality from evil intention, or which consist in a vio-

CRIMINAL CASES 

175 lation of those social duties which appertain to men and subjects. They cannot be guilty of treason or a felony or perjury or offenses against the person. They may be civilly liable for libel or malicious prosecution. They may be indicted for misfeasance, as well as for malfeasance. They can be guilty of criminal contempt.

On whom criminal summons may be served. 

Com. v. N. Y. C. R. R., 206 Mass. 417

The persons of a corporation on whom a criminal summons should be served.

Criminal liability of officers of a. 

Com. v. Dyer, 243 Mass. 472 

Com. v. Dow, 217 Mass. 473

Criminal liability of officers of a corporation and agents. 

Conversion and conspiracy. 

Indictments against. 

Com. v. Met. St. Ry., 107 Mass. 236

An indictment may be maintained against a street railway company for causing the death of a person. See Acts of 1864, Chapter 229, Section 37. 

Com. v. East Boston Ferry, 13 Allen, 589

A corporation may be indicted as a common carrier to recover a penalty for the loss of life. 

Com. v. Inhab. of Taunton, 16 Gray, 228

A complaint may be maintained against a town for suffering a highway to be out of repair. 

Com. v. Vermont & Mass. Ry., 4 Gray, 22 

Can be indicted for a nuisance. Libel. 

Colby Haberdashery v. Bradstreets, 267 Mass. 166

A may maintain an action for libel and the defendant may be liable for the publication of libelous matter written by a servant in the course of the business in which it is engaged. 

Finnish Society v. Finnish Co., 238 Mass. 345

A corporation can recover for libel on its character and reputation.

CRIMINAL CASES Charter. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 115 Mass. 153 

A corporation was chartered to manufacture liquor. A law was enacted making the manufacture and selling illegal. Although the legislature has no power to alter, modify, or repeal a charter, this provision does not allow a corporation to violate a law. Foreign. 

Ide v. Pierce, 134 Mass. 260

The books of a foreign corporation, copies verified and attached to a deposition, are admissible. 

COUNTY COMMISSIONERS 

Opinion of Justices, 167 Mass. 599

The county commissioner is not an officer of the Commonwealth and is not subject to impeachment. 

County of Norfolk v. Cook, 211 Mass. 390

Money paid voluntarily by a person in his own behalf under no mistake as to the facts cannot be recovered back. This rule does not apply to an action brought by a county to recover the amount of unauthorized payments made in good faith.

COURTS, MUNICIPAL AND DISTRICT, AND CLERKS OF THE SAME 

Courts of record. 

Com. v. Leach, 246 Mass. 464

District courts are courts of record and of superior and general jurisdiction with reference to all matters within. 

Finer v. Com., 250 Mass. 493 

District courts are courts of superior and general jurisdiction in all cases and matters in which they have jurisdiction, and the like presumption shall be made in favor of proceedings of such courts as would be made in favor of proceedings in other courts.

General Laws, Chapter 218, Section 4. 

Com. v. Hawkes, 123 Mass. 525 

Has concurrent jurisdiction with the Superior Court.

CRIMINAL CASES 

177 

Where held. 

Com. v. Carney, 152 Mass. 566

The court can be held anywhere in the county provided. 

When open for business. 

Tim v. Rosenfield, 168 Mass. at 394

The court shall always be open for business; the business of the court may be transacted at any time. 

Correcting mistakes and amending its records. 

Ferris v. St. Paul's Church, 220 Mass. 356

A court of record has ample power to correct mistakes in its record, by ordering the striking out of entries made by the clerk without authority. 

Haskell v. Cunningham, 221 Mass. 49

The records of the court cannot be changed by the clerk. 

They are conclusive and cannot be controlled by parol evidence. 

Com. v. Soderquest, 183 Mass. 199

The court has the power at all times to correct mistakes in the record of the court.

Distinction between court and justices of. 

Com. v. Gedzium, 261 Mass. 299

Distinction between acts required to be done by the court and by the justices of the court. 

Judicial notice. 

Brodsky v. Fine, 263 Mass. 51

While courts will take judicial notice of general laws and of public acts local in operation, they will not take cognizance of municipal ordinances or of special acts of the legislature. 

Justices of the peace. 

Meade v. Bowker, 168 Mass. 234

A court of record does not include the justices of the peace. 

Impounded property - Bail. 

Tuck v. Manning, 150 Mass. 211 

Money deposited for bail in the hands of the clerk is not liable to a legal or equitable attachment.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Wallace v. Coyne, 230 Mass. 475

Impounded property held by the court is not subject to a trustee process. Bail. 

Way v. Day, 187 Mass. 476

A defendant cannot assign money deposited in the court to a third person. 

Reed v. Police Court, 172 Mass. 427

Money deposited in court can be forfeited on the default of the defendant. On his recapture the situation is not changed. If he had surrendered himself the situation would have been different. 

Power of the court. 

H. B. Smith v. Judge of Dist. Ct., 246 Mass. 190 

White v. Gove, 183 Mass. 333

It is a general rule that when a case has been disposed of finally, the further power of the court to deal with it, save in the ways provided by statute, is gone. 

Com. v. Buck, 285 Mass. 41 

Klous v. Justices, 251 Mass. 293

A district court has the power to order a complaint dismissed after an indictment has been found in the superior court on the same facts. The case in the district court can be nol prossed by the district attorney, the effect of which is to cause its dismissal without a trial. 

Bryer v. Am. Surety Co., 285 Mass. 336

The record of a court imports verity. No parol evidence is admissible to amplify, modify, or contradict it in any collateral proceeding. Appropriate proceedings to correct mistakes or errors in such records are inherent in the court in which they are alleged to have occurred. The records as made up must be taken to speak the truth and cannot be varied by evidence. If the record of the court is untrue, the court has plenary power to make the record conform to the truth. 

Mandamus to compel. 

Channell v. Court, 213 Mass. 78

A writ of mandamus directed to a lower court is to compel it to exercise its judicial functions, not to direct what action will be taken.

CRIMINAL CASES 

179 

Mariano v. Justices, 243 Mass. 90

A writ of mandamus will not lie to allow a defendant to effect his appeal after a suspension of the sentence. The defendant was placed on probation under a suspended sentence; subsequently was surrendered. The sentence was reduced and he entered an appeal. It was then too late. 

Teehan v. Justices, 191 Mass. 92

A writ of prohibition will not issue to prohibit a lower court from deciding wrongly a question of its jurisdiction. 

Shuman v. Gilbert, 229 Mass. 225 

Harmon v. Police Com'r., 274 Mass. 56

It is well settled that while a court of equity, generally speaking, has no jurisdiction over the prosecution, the punishment, or the pardon of crimes or misdemeanors, a distinction obtains, and equitable jurisdiction exists to restrain criminal proceedings under unconstitutional enactments, when the prevention of such prosecutions is essential to the safeguarding of rights or property. 

Clerks of municipal and district courts. 

Com. v. Gay, 153 Mass. 211

The clerk may swear complaints when the court is not in session. A complaint to a district court can be sworn before a clerk pro tempore, and he can issue a warrant. 

Com. v. Possen, 182 Mass. 339

A trial justice who is also a clerk of the court can issue process returnable before one authorized to hear the same, but the clerk who issues a warrant as the clerk (he being an officer of the court) can make it returnable before his own court. 

French v. Neal, 41 Mass. 55

The clerk cannot take the records from the files. He is the agent of the court and has the custody of the records. They are in legal contemplation always on the files of the court. 

Com. v. Vincent, 165 Mass. 18

The clerk can amend records in the superior court at any time before the case is submitted to the jury. 

Austin v. Lamar Ins. Co., 108 Mass. 338

The omission of the clerk's signature to an original writ issued from his office is a defect which can be amended.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Tracey v. Noyes, 143 Mass. 449 

A complaint sworn to by the clerk carries the presumption that it was done while the court was in session. 

Thompson v. Boston Pub. Co., 285 Mass. 344

Under the rendition statute, Chapter 276, Section 16, the clerk has the power to issue process. 

Com. v. Mather, 121 Mass. 65

The 19th article of the state constitution, requiring the legislature to provide for the election of clerks of courts of the several counties, does not apply to the Municipal Court, which jurisdiction is inferior to the principal courts and extends over a part of a county only. 

Com. v. Barry, 115 Mass. 146 

The signature of the clerk followed by the word "clerk" is a sufficient attestation of a record sent to the superior court. 

Assistant clerks. 

Com. v. Wetherbee, 153 Mass. 159 

Com. v. Svarnas, 224 Mass. 597 

Com. v. Possen, 182 Mass. 339

Under General Laws, Chapter 218, Section 33, an assistant clerk of a district court has the authority to receive complaints addressed to the court and administer oaths. His office is created by law. 

Com. v. Gay, 153 Mass. 211 

Assistant clerks may sign where the law says that the clerk may sign. General Laws, Chapter 218, Section 33. 

Com. v. Crawford, 111 Mass. 422

An assistant clerk of a district court has the authority to attest copies of record transmitted to the superior court. 

Com. v. Brusie, 145 Mass. 117

Where the complaint is received to and sworn to before the justice and the warrant is issued by him, it is not necessary that the clerk should sign the jurat or the warrant or that the warrant should bear the teste of the justice of the court. These proceedings apply to proceedings before the court and not to those of a justice acting by the authority given him to be exercised when the court is not in session.

CRIMINAL CASES 

181 

Sullivan v. Wentworth, 137 Mass. 233

A constable may upon a capias issued by a district court in a criminal case arrest a person outside of the town for which he is elected, but in the same county and within the jurisdiction of the court issuing the warrant. 

Sworn to. 

Com. v. Calhane, 110 Mass. 498 "Received and sworn to on this 23d day of September 1872,

J. K. Jenness, Clerk." This sufficiently shows that in the absence of any evidence tending to prove the contrary, it was received and sworn to before the court, and when the clerk was authorized to certify to what took place. 

City of Taunton v. Sprout, 2 Gray, 428

The clerk is bound to pay over all fines to the city treasurer, and is not liable to actions by cities and towns to whose use the fines are appropriated by the statutes. 

Old Colony v. Thomas, 205 Mass. 529

Special papers transmitted by the lower court to the superior court must be filed in the clerk's office and nowhere else. 

Com. v. Meehan, 170 Mass. 362

A copy of record of a docket is competent to prove the facts stated in it if it has not been extended. 

Fogarty v. Connell, 153 Mass. 369 

An attest by a clerk at the end of a judgment imports that the papers are a copy of the record in the district court and includes the complaint and warrant. 

Com. v. Lynn, 154 Mass. 405

Defects in a complaint. Objections to a complaint and warrant for defects apparent on the face are waived by a failure to make them at the time when the defendant was arraigned in the district court. 

Easdale v. Reynolds, 143 Mass. 126

The docket is the record of the proceedings in that court until the record is extended, and is sufficient proof of those proceedings. When the originals are lost, secondary evidence is admissible and copies can be used in evidence.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Abuse by justice. 

Fisher v. Deans, 107 Mass. 118

The justice sentenced the defendant to pay a fine and costs and then permitted him to go at large for ten weeks, the fine and costs remaining unpaid. The justice then, because of some civil differences between them as citizens, committed him to jail. 

He is liable civilly.

CRUELTY TO ANIMALS GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 272, SECS. 77-95 

Com. v. Magoon, 172 Mass. 214

The defendant's guilt does not depend on whether he thought he was unnecessarily cruel, but whether he was so in fact. 

Com. v. Gentile, 255 Mass. 116

A dog was seen running away from a yard where a watchman was, in a blazing condition. The jury could find the defendant guilty. 

Com. v. Turner, 145 Mass. 296

A person who liberates a fox to be hunted by dogs may be convicted of subjecting animals to unnecessary cruelty. A captive fox is an animal within the meaning of the statute. 

Com. v. Wood, 111 Mass. 408

For cruelly overdriving. If the jury finds that in the proper exercise of the driver's own judgment he thought he was not overdriving, he is not guilty. If pain was inflicted in wanton and reckless disregard of suffering, then he is guilty. Must be knowingly and willfully. 

Com. v. Thornton, 113 Mass. 457

Having the charge and custody of a certain animal, etc., did knowingly and willfully authorize and permit the same to be subjected to unnecessary torture is not bad for ambiguity. 

Com. v. McClennan, 101 Mass. 34 "Did cruelly beat a horse" is a good averment. 

Com. v. Lufkin, 7 Allen, 579 

What the word "cruel" means.

CRIMINAL CASES 

183 

Com. v. Whitman, 118 Mass. 458 "Did then and there torture and torment the said cow by then and there cruelly beating, bruising, and wounding the said cow." This is sufficient. Need not allege he had the care and custody; if so alleged can be rejected. Need not allege the ownership or that the cruelty was unnecessary. 

Com. v. Flannigan, 137 Mass. 560 "Did cruelly overdrive a certain horse" is good. 

Com. v. Curry, 150 Mass. 509

Unnecessarily failed to provide a horse hired by him and in his custody with proper food, drink, and protection from the weather for the space of 24 hours thereafter. Good complaint. 

Com. v. Edmands, 162 Mass. 517

Pleading continuando for having the charge and custody of a certain animal, to wit, a horse, and it was then and during the whole time aforesaid the duty of the said Edmands to provide the said horse with proper shelter and protection from the weather, and that the said Edmands did then and during the time aforesaid unnecessarily and cruelly fail to provide the said horse with proper shelter and protection from the weather, etc., sufficiently alleges the offense. The word "cruelly" is not material and may be rejected as surplusage. 

Com. v. Porter, 164 Mass. 576

An averment in a complaint that the defendant did then and there cruelly drive a certain horse when unfit for labor as aforesaid. The pleader need not allege that the defendant knew the horse to be unfit for labor as aforesaid.

No indictment can be maintained against the keeper of a billiard room for refusing to allow a negro to play therein, unless the billiard room is licensed.

If the defendant pleaded in court and was fined, an agreement that if he did so no suit would be instituted bars a recovery on the civil side of the court. 

DISORDERLY HOUSE, KEEPING A COMMON LAW GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 279, SEC. 5 

Com. v. Goodall, 165 Mass. 588

A disorderly house is a resort for thieves, drunks and prostitutes, or other idle and disorderly and vicious persons. The

CRIMINAL CASES 

185 common law is not repealed by Chapter 139 of the General 

Laws. 

Com. v. Bagdasarian, 257 Mass. 248

A conversation with an inmate of a disorderly house in the absence of the defendant in regard to bargaining is admissible against the defendant. 

Com. v. Bulman, 118 Mass. 456

Keeping a single room can be complained of for the keeping of a disorderly house. 

Com. v. Davenport, 2 Allen, 299

The commonwealth need not prove that all persons residing near or passing were annoyed, but it would be sufficient to prove a frequent annoyance and disturbance during the time alleged large numbers of disorderly persons going in and coming out, conducting themselves in a disorderly manner, although witnesses did not see what went on inside the house. 

Com. v. O'Brien, 8 Gray, 487

Evidence of doors being broken while the defendant occupied the house is admissible. Proof of quarreling and fighting, the breaking in of persons whom the defendant was trying to keep out, and the breaking out of persons whom the defendant was trying to keep in. 

Com. v. Hopkins, 133 Mass. 381

A complaint may be maintained by proof that only one person in the neighborhood was disturbed or annoyed, if the acts done were of such a nature as tended to annoy all citizens. 

Com. v. Cardoze, 119 Mass. 210

Evidence is admissible of resorting to by men and women of bad repute, that women made assignments with men and would depart with them, that the defendant was present at the dance hall which he ran. The habitual meeting of such persons would tend to produce riotous and disorderly conduct. 

Com. v. Cobb, 120 Mass. 356

During some substantial part of the time alleged in the complaint in whole or in part, the control and management while the defendant kept it, it was a common, ill-governed, and disorderly house. Suffering it to be frequented by prostitutes, thieves, and drunkards at unreasonable hours. It may consist of allowing

CRIMINAL CASES 

it to be noisy (the specific noise need not be proved) so as to attract those who would disturb the neighborhood. 

Com. v. Wise, 110 Mass. 181

A complaint charging the defendant with keeping a noisy and disorderly tenement is. void, because "tenement," although it includes a house or building, has a much more enlarged signification. It signifies land or any corporeal inheritance or anything of a permanent nature which may be holden.

The signature of a district attorney is not essential to the validity of an indictment.

Form of proceeding for the removal of a district attorney by information. In the above case the district attorney was confined in an insane asylum.

The district attorney can testify as to what took place in the grand jury room when the time has passed that secrecy is important.

DISTURBING THE PEACE COMMON LAw GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 279, SEC. 5 

Com. v. Porter, 1 Gray, 476 

An assembly includes meetings for temperance, political gatherings, meeting for amusement, and all public meetings held for public purposes. Coughing, whistling, or talking in a loud and boisterous manner is enough. 

Com. v. Smith, 6 Cush. 80

It is not an offense to utter loud cries in the public streets to the great disturbance of divers citizens. Such acts constitute a nuisance, and the pleader must allege it to be to the great damage and common nuisance of all citizens.

CRIMINAL CASES 

187 

Com. v. Hoxey, 16 Mass. 385

Disturbance of a town meeting is an offense at common law.

Outcries uttered in a public street, although they disturb but a single person, if they are accompanied by other circumstances necessary to constitute the offense, are an indictable offense. 

Com. v. Harris, 101 Mass. 29 

Uttering loud outcries and loud exclamations in a public street. A citizen had a loud argument with a constable, using profane language and epithets, drawing a large crowd who shouted back words of encouragement.

Licenses. 

DOGS GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 140, SECS. 137-175 ACTS OF 1932, CHAP. 289 

Com. v. Coates, 169 Mass. 354 

A complaint for keeping an unlicensed dog may be pleaded continuando. 

Com. v. Palmer, 134 Mass. 537 

Com. v. Thompson, 2 Allen, 507

The defendant, who summered in Winthrop, had the dog licensed. The statute required in such case that the defendant must have the license transferred to there while he was staying there. 

Com. v. Wermouth, 174 Mass. 74 

Applying nine days after receiving a dog is an unreasonable delay. 

Com. v. Canada, 107 Mass. 405 

Com. v. Jones, 15 Gray, 193

The owner or keeper must license the dog, but if it is not done by either the penalty falls on the keeper only. 

Com. v. Brimblecomb, 4 Allen, 584 

One who purchases an unlicensed dog after April 1 of any year is not subject to a penalty for the omission to have it licensed.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Brahaney, 123 Mass. 245

A license for a yellow and white dog named Dixie will not authorize the keeping of a black Newfoundland dog named Nig. 

Larceny of. 

Com. v. Flynn, 285 Mass. 136 

The words "domesticated animal" in Chapter 266, Section 46, of the General Laws includes a dog whether licensed or unlicensed, and indicates an intention of the legislature in passing the act to include any dog as the subject of larceny. 

The fact that the owner has not complied with the statutory provisions as to its keeping and licensing gives no right to another to take away the dog and keep it as his own. 

Blair v. Forehand, 100 Mass. 136

Under the common law a person had no property in a dog and it was not the subject of larceny. 

Collared and licensed. 

Moore v. Willis, 191 Mass. 56 

If a dog wears a collar and the wrong number of his license, he is not collared under the provisions of the statute. 

Cummings v. Perham, 1 Met. 555 

The provision that any person may kill a dog which is not collared does not authorize a person to convert such dog to his own use, and a person so converting such dog is liable in an action of trover. 

Trespasser upon the highway. 

Bear v. Tyler, 261 Mass. 138 

A dog leashed but not collared or licensed is not an outlaw on the public highway, and if injured an action will lie. General 

Laws, Chapter 140, Section 151, relating to the clause "caused to be killed," is to be construed strictly. 

Laxver v. Strauss, 226 Mass. 579

An unlicensed dog is not a trespasser upon the highway, and the owner can recover when the injury is caused by the willful and negligent operation of an automobile.

CRIMINAL CASES 

189 

Going at large. 

Com. v. Dow, 10 Met. 382

A dog is going at large if he be loose and following the person who has charge of him through the streets of the town, and at such a distance that he cannot exercise control over him which will prevent his doing mischief. 

The killing of. 

Morewood v. Wakefield, 133 Mass. 240

Under a statute which directs that all officers shall, and all others may, kill an unlicensed and uncollared dog, a citizen can kill a dog which is licensed but has no collar on, providing he can do so without committing a trespass, although such killing is before July 1 and no warrant had been issued for the killing. 

Cozzens v. Mason, 109 Mass. 275 

Blair v. Freehand, 100 Mass. 136

A constable has no right to enter a dwelling house without express or implied authority of the owner to execute a warrant for the killing of an unlicensed dog, and one who does so even if the door is open is a trespasser. 

Uhlien v. Cromack, 109 Mass. 273 

One who keeps a dog for the protection of his family, duly licensed and collared so as not to endanger persons on his messuage, may recover its value from a neighbor who killed it without being attacked by it, although it was a dangerous animal and accustomed to bite those who came near it. 

McAnenay v. Jewett, 10 Allen, 151 

Tower v. Tower, 18 Pick. 262

A dog at play with the owner's son upon the owner's land is not at large, and a constable who under these circumstances calls him away and shoots him while on the owner's land and enters upon such land for the purpose of shooting him again is liable. 

Ker v. Seaver, 11 Allen, 151. 

Bishop v. Fahey, 15 Gray, 61 

The act authorizing anyone to kill an unlicensed dog wherever found does not authorize the entry into the dwelling house of another without his express or implied consent for the purpose of killing such dog which had taken refuge there.

CRIMINAL CASES DOMICILE 

See Husband and wife. 

Hayes v. Hayes, 256 Mass. 97

Living together as husband and wife in this state means that the parties have acquired a domicile; the living together for one night does not establish a domicile. 

Viles v. City of Waltham, 157 Mass. 542 

To acquire a domicile, there must be a residence in that place and an intention to make that place their home. 

Com. v. Bogigian, 265 Mass. 531

One's domicile is not lost until another one is acquired. 

Winans v. Winans, 205 Mass. 388

A couple who live in this state for two weeks with the intention of finding a home here, then go away, have acquired a domicile here. 

Kopigian v. Der Minassian, 212 Mass. 412

A person whose domicile at a certain time is in issue may testify as to his intention at that time. 

Feehan v. Trefry, 237 Mass. 169

The domicile of any person is mainly a question of fact.

The excessive use of narcotic drugs constituting drunkenness in a libel for divorce discussed.

Arrest for.

DRUNKENNESS GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 272, SECS. 44 49 

Com. v. Conlin, 184 Mass. 195

One may be convicted of drunkenness although the offense was committed in a private dwelling and was not causing a public disturbance. 

Ford v. Breen, 173 Mass. 52

One can be arrested without a warrant. The door of the dwelling house being open, the officer had the right to enter without an invitation. An officer cannot without a warrant lawfully arrest on the charge of drunkenness unless the person is drunk at the time of the arrest. 

Com. v. O'Connor, 7 Allen, 583

There is no authority to arrest for the mere crime of drunkenness; it is only under Section 44 of Chapter 272 of the General 

Laws where a person is in a public place, or while intoxicated commits a breach of the peace. Pleading. 

Com. v. McNamara, 116 Mass. 340

The form for drunkenness in the allegation was "guilty of the crime of drunkenness." Is a good allegation but not one to be commended. 

Entering a house and arresting one for. 

Ford v. Breen, 173 Mass. 52 

Com. v. Conlin, 184 Mass. 195 

Com. v. Tobin, 108 Mass. 426

An officer entered an unlocked house and found a man assaulting his wife. The defendant was drunk. While the officer was holding him he resisted the officer, who thereupon arrested him for resisting an officer and for assault. The fact that the officer did not lodge a complaint against him for drunkenness is not a defense for the arrest, as the affray and assault are a disturbance of the peace. 

Making the complaint. 

Horgan v. B. E. Ry., 208 Mass. 287

A complaint means an oral obligation by an officer which is reduced to writing in proper form by the court; and unless the

CRIMINAL CASES 

officer complies with these requirements he is civilly liable, the arrest is only preliminary. 

Popineau v. Bacon, 110 Mass. 319

One who arrests a person for drunkenness must take him before a justice in the town in which he is arrested. 

Garney v. Parkman, 100 Mass. 316

The officer who arrests the person is not bound personally to make the complaint against him; the statute is complied with if another person makes it. Sentence. 

Com. v. Morrissey, 157 Mass. 471

The provision that a person arrested for drunkenness if he can satisfy the court that he has not been arrested twice within a year can be placed on file is constitutional. This fact does not take away the right of the court to sentence for the first offense. 

Whether voluntary. 

Com. v. Coughlin, 123 Mass. 436 

Com. v. Hughes, 133 Mass. 496 

Com. v. Dale, 264 Mass. 535

The defendant was found staggering in the street behaving in a drunken manner, breath smelling of liquor. The defendant admitted this but argued that the state must show that the drunkenness was caused by the voluntary use of intoxicating liquor. It is true that a man can be made drunk by having liquor forced upon him without his consent, and if so, he would not be guilty of drunkenness. If nothing further appears except that he was drunk, he could not be convicted. The attending circumstances must be taken into consideration and it is for the jury to determine whether the drunkenness was voluntary or not.

Drunkenness as an excuse. 

Com. v. Taylor, 263 Mass. 356 

Com. v. Parsons, 195 Mass. 560 

Com. v. Gilbert, 165 Mass. 45

Drunkenness is no excuse. One who is so intoxicated as to be overcome cannot be convicted of first-degree murder.

CRIMINAL CASES 

193 

Com. v. Malone, 114 Mass. 295 

Com. v. Hawkins, 69 Mass. 463 

Com. v. Hagenblock, 140 Mass. 125

The rule of law is, that although the use of intoxicating liquor does to some extent blind the reason and exasperate the passions, yet, when a man voluntarily brings it on himself, he cannot use it as an excuse or justification or extenuation of crime. 

A man because he is intoxicated is not deprived of any legal advantage or protection, but he cannot avail himself of his intoxication to exempt him from any legal responsibility which would attach to him if sober.

Drunkenness on a passenger car. 

Pedsik v. Worcester St. Ry., 216 Mass. 213

A conductor of a street railway car has the right to eject one from the car who is visibly intoxicated. 

Murphy v. B. &. M. R. R., 216 Mass. 178 

If a passenger is drunk, unable to pay his fare or tender a fare or tickets, the servants of the company may remove him from the car, and when so done he ceases to be a passenger. 

Evidence. 

Phillips v. Fadden, 125 Mass. 198

An officer who arrests a person for drunkenness on the grounds that he was found in a state of intoxication is liable civilly if he fails to make a complaint against him or if the person is not in fact drunk, although the officer acts in good faith. If the person and officer agree and understand after he is arrested and allowed to go home by the officer that no further prosecution will be brought, the officer is not civilly liable. 

Burt v. Burt, 168 Mass. at 206 

Com. v. Barber, 261 Mass. 281

Whether a person is drunk is a question which a person not an expert is competent to answer. It is something which may fairly be considered to be a matter of common knowledge and observation. 

Com. v. Curry, 150 Mass. 509

If one gets bewildered because he voluntarily got intoxicated the fact that he got bewildered is not a defense. If he got bewildered from other causes, the fact might be a defense.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Cheney, 141 Mass. 102

A police officer was indicted for assault and battery on A. 

A was arrested for drunkenness. The record of A's conviction the day after the arrest cannot be used in the case of assault and battery to show that A was drunk. 

Com. v. Miller, 8 Gray, 484

In a room in another person's house (a room in the defendant's house) drunkenness is punishable without proof that the drunkenness was made public. 

Com. v. Presby, 14 Gray, 65

A police officer arresting one for drunkenness without a warrant is not criminally liable for assault and battery, even if in fact the person arrested was not in fact intoxicated. 

Common drunkard. 

Com. v. Conley, 1 Allen, 6

Proof of habitual intoxication will support an indictment for being a common drunkard without proof of any disturbance of the public peace and good order. 

Com. v. Whitney, 5 Gray, 85

To convict for drunkenness it is at least necessary to show that he is a habitual drunkard. The terms "drunkard" and "habitual drunkard" are the same. The word "common" would seem to be used in the sense of public. 

Com. v. Boon, 2 Gray, 74

The pleader need not conclude the indictment "to the common nuisance of all citizens" because it is not in the nature of a nuisance. 

Com. v. McNamee, 112 Mass. 285

From August 15 to December 7, from five to seven times on as many days between above dates, makes one a common drunkard.

Arrested for drunk and assault and battery. 

Com. v. Coughlin, 123 Mass. 436

Arrested without a warrant for drunkenness. Commits assault and battery on officer. Acquitted for drunk, but may be convicted of assault and battery, such acquittal not being con-

CRIMINAL CASES 

195 clusive evidence that he was not drunk when arrested nor that the officer was not in the discharge of his duty in making the arrest.

DUCES TECUM 

Com. v. Ryan, 157 Mass. 403

Duces tecum papers can be impounded by the court. 

Kincaide v. Cavanaugh, 198 Mass. 34

A witness has papers in court but not by virtue of a duces tecum subpoena. The court can compel him to produce them without such subpoena.

DWELLING HOUSE 

Com. v. Barney, 10 Cush. 480

It must be a place of residence of the party named, as in an indictment for arson or breaking and entering. The house may for the time being be in fact without inmates and the occupants temporarily absent, but there must be the purpose to return. But it cannot be a dwelling house of an individual before he has begun to occupy it. 

Com. v. Buzzell, 16 Pick. 153

An outbuilding not connected with the dwelling house is not part of the dwelling house. 

Com. v. Estabrook, 10 Pick. 293

A small building on the same lot as the dwelling house, 45 rods distant with a passageway between them, is not an apartment or dependence of the dwelling house, though the same person occupies the whole lot, including the house and the building. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 6 Allen, 599 "A house" without a further description is generally understood to be a dwelling house; and a house occupied by a man is generally understood to be that in which he dwells. 

Com. v. Barney, 64 Mass. 478

A building which has never been occupied by the owner nor by any person for many months is not a "dwelling house" so as to be the subject of arson.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Squire, 42 Mass. 258

The word "building" as used in the arson statute does not necessarily import a structure so far advanced as to be in every respect finished and perfect for the purpose for which it is ultimately to be used.

DYING DECLARATIONS GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 233, SEC. 64 GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 272, SEC. 19 

Com. v. Wagner, 231 Mass. 265

The declaration is admissible whether it is oral or written. 

Com. v. Bishop, 165 Mass. 148

The existence of any expectation of recovery, however slight, renders the declaration inadmissible.

A mother whose daughter has been operated upon and is in fear of death may testify to dying declarations.

It is a question of fact for the jury as to whether the woman knew that she was going to die.

It is a question for the jury as to whether the dying man felt he was to die or that he had hopes of recovery.

197 

Com. v. Dunan, 128 Mass. 422

A statement of the deceased is not admissible unless it can be shown that it was a dying declaration. 

Com. v. Haney, 127 Mass. 455

The statement of the deceased, suffering from an injury from which he died four days later, is admissible. It is no objection to its admission that the statement was made in answer to questions and was reduced to writing in his presence and subscribed and sworn to him by him. 

Com. v. Cooper, 5 Allen, 495

Dying declarations made by the deceased, death occurring seventeen days later, are admissible.

A, being at the point of death and conscious of her condition but unable to speak, was asked whether it was B who had inflicted the wounds, and if so she was directed to squeeze the hand of the person asking the question. She then squeezed the hand of that person. Admissible. The sole question for the jury and the effect to be given to it.

ESCAPE 

GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 268, SECS. 16-21 

From an institution. 

Com. v. Larochelle, 264 Mass. 490 

Com. v. Curley, 101 Mass. 24

A prison camp and hospital to which a prisoner has been transferred from a penal institution is itself a penal institution within the meaning of the statute. A plot of ground used for recreational purposes is "land appurtenant to a penal institution."

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Barker, 133 Mass. 399

One who has been arrested on mesne process, admitted to bail and afterwards surrendered by his bail to the keeper of the jail is lawfully imprisoned, and if he forcibly escapes from the jail, he may be convicted under the statute. 

From an officer. 

Whitehead v. Keyes, 1 Allen, 350

An officer after an escape may retake the escaping party on fresh pursuit. 

Com. v. Ferrell, 5 Allen, 130

An escape from the custody of an officer who had him by virtue of a mittimus is an escape from an officer at common law. Now see Acts 1934, Chapter 344. 

Com. v. Andrews, 97 Mass. 543

A defendant in a criminal case who while his exceptions are pending escapes from jail waives all rights that he is entitled to. 

Com. v. Morihan, 86 Mass. 585

An officer's custody of a prisoner whom he has arrested upon a warrant and brought before a court for trial does not cease until the prisoner has been discharged or a warrant of commitment made out. 

Com. v. Homer, 46 Mass. 555 

Com. v. Briggs, 46 Mass. 555

The Acts of 1934, Chapter 344, apply only to prisoners who are sentenced to a term of imprisonment and who are confined in pursuance thereof, and does not apply to a prisoner who is held in custody for trial or for not obtaining bail for his appearance. 

Com. v. Filburn, 119 Mass. 297

In a complaint for aiding a prisoner to escape, General Laws, Chapter 268, Section 17, the pleader must set forth the acts done by the defendant; merely following the words of the statute are not enough. 

Com. v. Morihan, 86 Mass. 585

It is no defense to a complaint charging the defendant with aiding in the escape of a prisoner that the complaint or sentence under which he was held was irregular.
