EVIDENCE 

Proof differing from the date alleged in complaint. 

Com. v. Robinson, 165 Mass. 426

An assault was charged in the complaint as taking place on 

August 2. Proof of the assault was of the 3d or 1st of August. 

Admissible and will support complaint. 

Com. v. Lindsay, 223 Mass. 392

The commonwealth can introduce evidence of the sale of liquor on days other than those named in the complaint. 

Com. v. Koslowski, 252 Mass. 149

Liquor was seized on the 8th of April. The defendant admitted that the liquor that he had on hand March 6 was kept for sale. The jury could infer that it was his intention to sell that on April 8. 

Com. v. Sacco, 255 Mass. 369

Evidence is admissible that witnesses saw the defendant fourteen days before the commission of the crime in an automobile that answered the description of the automobile used in the crime. 

Com. v. Piccerillo, 256 Mass. 487

Evidence of undue familiarity before and after the alleged intimacy is admissible to show the adulterous disposition of the parties. Evidence before to show disposition on the part of the defendant and inclination and disposition of the defendant to commit the act charged. See, in connection, 242 Mass. 583. 

Com. v. Bean, 137 Mass. 570

One month previous to the date of the trial the defendant took the girl in question. Five weeks after the assault and before the arrest of the defendant he followed her on one occasion. Ad-

CRIMINAL CASES 

missible for the purpose of showing a continuing lustful intent. 

Com. v. Blassman, 253 Mass. 65

A variance between the date of the complaint and the proof is immaterial. 

Com. v. Sullivan, 123 Mass. 221

A witness for the state testified that he bought liquor at the defendant's house. He could not say when, but he thought within a year and that there was a hearing before a magistrate. 

Other witnesses fixed the time of the sale. Admissible. 

Com. v. Cleary, 152 Mass. 491

Evidence of the acts of the defendant after the seizure of liquor admissible. 

Com. v. Baldwin, 129 Mass. 481

Time is never necessary to be proved as laid, except where it is an element of the offense. It is not one of the rights of the defendant to be acquitted of a crime solely upon the ground that a witness for the state has made a mistake in stating the day of the month. The defendant was charged with committing an abortion on the 7th, and the crime was actually committed on the 

9th. The defendant had an alibi for the 7th. 

Laine v. Arnio, 265 Mass. 374

In computing time from the date or from the day of the date, or from a certain act or event, the day of the date is to be excluded, unless a different intention is manifested by the instrument or statute under which the question arises. 

Com. v. Carney, 152 Mass. 566 

Evidence produced on October 8th, the date of the signing of the complaint. Liquor that was in the safe and delivered to the officer after the date of the complaint is admissible. 

Com. v. Patalone, 254 Mass. 69

A convict can be convicted only when proved guilty of the crime within the period alleged in the complaint when averred continuando. 

Com. v. Elwell, 1 Gray, 463

On the first day of January did presume to be a common seller of liquor. As this is a continuous offense, evidence of sales must be confined to that day alone.

CRIMINAL CASES 

201 Intent. 

Com. v. McCarthy, 119 Mass. 354

The commonwealth can show the question of intent that on an indictment for the burning of a building September 10th that on August 24 and September 6 the defendant set fire to a shed ten feet distant from the building and connected to it by a flight of steps. 

Com. v. Closson, 229 Mass. 329

In prosecution for misdemeanors created by statute under police power proof of a guilty mind or corrupt purpose is not essential for a conviction. 

Com. v. Lavery, 188 Mass. 13

Assuming to be a common victualer, one carrying on the business under another name not an agent, even if he honestly believed that he had the right, is guilty as no intent is necessary. 

Com. v. Phelps, 209 Mass. 396

Adulterated milk. No intent necessary; guilty knowledge is not essential. 

Com. v. Mixer, 207 Mass. 141

Collection of cases dealing with criminal offenses created by statute in which innocence of intent is no defense. 

Com. v. Peakes, 231 Mass. 449

A belief that one has the right to resort to forgery and larceny in collecting debts which the firm owed the defendant does not deprive forgery of its criminal character nor excuse its commission. An authorization will not purge criminality. An intent to defraud is a general intent to defraud anyone. 

Com. v. Ladd, 15 Mass. 526

It is sufficient to prove a general intent to defraud someone; it is not necessary for the state to prove that anyone was actually defrauded. 

Com. v. N. C., 202 Mass. 394

In statutory offenses, unless a wrongful intent designated by willfulness or maliciousness is made the essential element, the violator may be convicted as in adulterated milk cases. 

Com. v. Hagenblock, 140 Mass. 125

It is a question of fact for the jury to determine whether one is so far intoxicated as to be unable to form a guilty intent.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Telegram v. Com., 172 Mass. 294

Corporations. Where a specific intent may be a necessary element a corporation may be liable criminally. See under Corporation. 

Com. v. Cutler, 153 Mass. 252

From the mere fact that mortgaged property was conveyed without the consent of the mortgagee, the intent may be inferred. 

Com. v. Hawkins, 157 Mass. 551

The defendant discharged a pistol in a street in a reckless manner, not aiming at anyone, and someone was injured. No intent is necessary where one intends to do an unlawful act which is punishable because of wrong in itself, and in so doing inflicts unforeseen injury. He is criminally liable for the charge, the fact that it is done in violation of a revised ordinance is proper evidence for the jury to consider on the question of negligence. 

Com. v. Cali, 247 Mass. 20

Intent can be shown that after a fire has started accidentally and the owner could have put it out but let it burn, the intent could be formed then to defraud the insurance company. 

Com. v. Taylor, 263 Mass. 356

No matter how drunk a man may be and no matter what the intention may be, the law does not permit drunkenness under such circumstances to be an excuse for a crime. If the jury is satisfied that the defendant was incapable of conceiving a deliberate premeditation, he is not guilty of first degree but of second degree, that is, if he is so overcome. 

Com. v. Derby, 263 Mass. 39

In a complaint for assault and battery with intent to rape, the jury can take into consideration the consciousness of guilt in determining whether there was an intention to rape. 

Com. v. Emmons, 98 Mass. 6 

Admitting a minor to a pool room: need not aver nor prove guilty intent of the defendant. If the minor present and suffered to remain, either by the defendant or his servants or agents, even if the defendant forbade them and he was not present when they did enter, he is guilty.

CRIMINAL CASES 

203 

Com. v. Presby, 14 Gray, 65

Without a criminal intent there is no offense, but this intent is always inferred from the unlawful act (4 Gray, 38); but unlawful act means here intentional violence without justification or excuse, and where ignorance of a fact is an excuse the inference is rebutted. 

Com. v. Jackson, 132 Mass. 16

Evidence of other sales of horses similar to the one the defendant is charged with selling is inadmissible. They are separate and distinct crimes from the one in question. 

Com. v. Bakeman, 105 Mass. 53

On the theory that a man must be presumed to intend the obvious and natural consequences of his voluntary act it is competent for the jury to infer that he did the act with intent. 

Com. v. Adams, 114 Mass. 323

If an act a party is doing is malum prohibitum he shall not be punished for any act arising from misfortune or mistake, but if malum in se it is otherwise. 

Com. v. McLaughlin, 105 Mass. 460

To attempt to commit a crime that is willful and malicious one imports an intent to commit that crime. 

Com. v. N. Y. C. Ry., 202 Mass. 394

In a prosecution for misdemeanors created by statute under police power proof of a guilty mind or corrupt purpose is not essential to a conviction. 

Com. v. Shepard, 1 Allen, 575

Evidence of another act of embezzlement committed by the defendant in the same week with the one charged against him is competent only for the purpose of proving guilty intent on his part in the commission of the principal act. It is a dangerous species of evidence, quotes Bigelow, C. J. 

Com. v. Shea, 150 Mass. 314

The statute expressly forbids the keeping of a house of ill fame. Whether the defendant intended to violate the statute or not, if she committed the act, she is guilty. 

Com. v. Reed, 150 Mass. 67

Selling leased property before acquiring the title thereto shows an intent to defraud.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Wentworth, 118 Mass. 441

Guilty knowledge is not essential to the ingredients of an offense of selling and keeping naphtha under an assumed name. 

Com. v. Adamian, 252 Mass. 105 

Fraud in the packing of coal. General Laws, Chapter 94, Section 305. It is no defense that the defendant packed the coal just as he received it from the owner. It contained shale, etc. No intent necessary.

Evidence illegally obtained admissible. 

Com. v. Tibbetts, 157 Mass. 519

Evidence which is pertinent to the issue is admissible, although it may have been procured in an irregular and illegal manner. Under a liquor search warrant the officer seized abortion implements. 

Com. v. Hartford, 193 Mass. 464

Procuring a card by lying and deceit does not vitiate the admission of it. It is a question for the jury whether it was delivered voluntarily with a criminal purpose. 

Com. v. Wilkins, 243 Mass. 356 

Com. v. Dana, 2 Met. 329

The 4th amendment of the Constitution does not apply to the courts of this state. A police officer seized liquor from the pocket of the defendant. No matter how the evidence is obtained, it still is admissible in court on the trial of the case. See also 

161 Mass. 61, and 155 Mass. 284. 

Of other crimes and schemes. 

Com. v. Leventhal, 236 Mass. 516

Evidence tending to prove similar but distinct crimes committed by the defendant is not admissible to show that he is guilty of the crime charged. To show the purpose and intent with which the crime was committed, evidence of previous acts indicating a disposition controlling the defendant in the commission of the crime is admissible. 

Com. v. Cali, 21 Pick. 515

If one offense is not admissible for the purpose of proving the charge of another, yet it may be so connected with the proof of a relevant and material fact that its introduction cannot be avoided.

CRIMINAL CASES 

205 

Com. v. Blood, 141 Mass. 571 

Com. v. Scott, 123 Mass. 222

Letters introduced to show a scheme to defraud. The fact that they disclosed other schemes had been consummated does not render them incompetent. 

Com. v. Danton, 243 Mass. 552

At a trial for larceny evidence of other crimes cannot be introduced to show that the defendant committed other crimes disconnected with the one on trial. 

Com. v. Fecci, 235 Mass. 562

If it be shown that the defendant committed other crimes, the commission of which has a tendency to establish the fact in controversy, such evidence plainly is admissible. 

Com. v. Snell, 189 Mass. 12

Evidence of other crimes is admissible to prove commission of crime charged when it tends to prove the absence of mistake or accident and when it tends to identify the person who committed the other crime as the same person as charged in the indictment. 

Com. v. Lubinsky, 182 Mass. 142

Evidence of a general scheme to defraud may be introduced for the purpose of showing a plan or scheme to show the intent under the indictment. 

Com. v. Dow, 217 Mass. 473

Evidence of a part of a general scheme is admissible. 

Com. v. Rivet, 205 Mass. 464

The fact that a person's habits and character are such that he would be apt to do the act is not competent evidence that he did the act. 

Com. v. Quinn, 222 Mass. 504

Evidence of a common scheme is admissible, but the state must show that the person was defrauded. 

Com. v. Corcoran, 252 Mass. 465

Evidence of a general scheme, tending to show that on previous occasions the same methods were used, is admissible even though all the defendants were not in the previous cases.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Bradford, 126 Mass. 42

The state can show that the defendant who was tried for burning a building had tried to burn the same building three days previous. It does not come within the provision of "similar offenses." 

Refreshing recollection. 

Com. v. Burton, 183 Mass. 461

It is not necessary for the writing to have been made by the witness himself or that it should be the original writing, provided that after inspection the witness can speak to the facts from his own recollection. 

Gurley v. Street Ry., 206 Mass. 534

A witness may refresh his recollection from records made by him although the same are not in evidence. 

Com. v. Jeffs, 132 Mass. 5

A witness may be allowed to refresh his memory by looking at a written or printed paper, and if he thereby recalls a fact or circumstance, he may testify to it. It is not the memorandum which is in evidence but the recollection of the witness. The paper cannot be put in evidence. 

Com. v. McDermott, 255 Mass. 575

A witness can refresh his memory from a paper. It is immaterial that the paper is not contemporaneous with the events it records or that it was not made by the testifying witness. 

Com. v. Tircinski, 189 Mass. 257

One on trial for manslaughter. Evidence of the general character of the deceased as a fighting man is admissible to show that the defendant feared bodily harm. 

Com. v. Lane, 151 Mass. 356 

Complaint for a nuisance from September 1 to September 9, the jury can refresh testimony from record sent up on appeal from the lower court as to the date of the trial. 

Reputation and character. 

Com. v. Tircinski, 189 Mass. 257

One on trial for manslaughter. Evidence of the general character of the deceased as a fighting man is admissible to show the defendant had reasons to fear bodily harm.

CRIMINAL CASES 

207 

Com. v. Baxter, 267 Mass. 591

Proof of character can be shown only by evidence of truth and veracity in the community, as disclosed by the common speech of the neighbors and members of the community. A person sent to the neighborhood to ascertain cannot testify as to the results of his inquiries. 

Com. v. O'Brien, 119 Mass. 342

When a person accused of a criminal offense introduces evidence to show that his general reputation is good, it is competent for the state in reply to put in evidence of particular facts. 

Com. v. Eagan, 151 Mass. 45

The defendant's witness was asked, "What was the general reputation of the place or tenement kept by the defendant in the neighborhood?" Inadmissible. 

Com. v. Bean, 111 Mass. 438

The defendant charged with indecent assault and battery can introduce testimony between him and the assaulted party, that subsequent to the crime she had asked the defendant to kiss her. 

Com. v. Gannett, 1 Allen, 7

In an indictment for keeping a house of ill fame the bad character for chastity of the women who frequent the house is competent in support of the indictment against the owner. 

Com. v. Billings, 97 Mass. 405

Evidence is admissible of general reputation for truth eighteen months previous to the trial. 

Com. v. Whittaker, 131 Mass. 224

In an indictment for enticing a woman of chaste life and conversation to enter a house of ill fame, the burden is on the state to prove the chastity of the woman. 

Com. v. Harris, 131 Mass. 336

In a prosecution for rape, evidence of particular instances of unchastity on the part of the woman with persons other than the defendant is admissible. The character of the woman for chastity is involved in the issue and may be impeached by general evidence of her reputation, but particular instances of criminal connection with other persons are inadmissible.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Kendall, 113 Mass. 210

In a complaint for indecent assault and battery, evidence of the chastity of the woman is admissible to show her character, in order that the jury may know as to her consent. 

Expert. 

Jewett v. B. E. Ry., 219 Mass. 528

A medical examiner's report is not admissible in evidence for the purpose of showing the cause of death. 

Whipple v. Grandshampe, 261 Mass. 40

An X-ray specialist or one who gives expert testimony on medical or surgical anatomical questions, even if he is not a doctor of medicine nor licensed or registered, can give expert evidence. 

Com. v. Brayman, 136 Mass. 438

On the issue of a mental condition or the capacity of a person at a particular time, a witness not an expert may be asked if he had noticed any difference in such a person's mode of doing business at the time and at a previous time. He cannot be asked his opinion as to the mental capacity at a certain time. 

Com. v. O'Brien, 134 Mass. 198

A witness may give his opinion as to the age of a person. 

Hill. v. Eldredge, 126 Mass. 234 

One may testify from his own knowledge of himself whether he is 16 or 21 years old at a certain time. 

Com. v. Leone, 250 Mass. 512

As to the speed of an automobile. The speed of a car when 

400 feet from the scene of the accident can be testified to by a person not an expert. 

Com. v. Sturtevant, 117 Mass. 122

Every person is qualified to express an opinion on a question of identity as applied to persons, things, animals or handwriting. Collection of cases on above. Witness can read extracts from a work on medical jurisprudence to the court or jury. He may show a person to be left handed. 

Com. v. Pope, 103 Mass. 440

One who is not an expert may testify as to shoes and footprints, as this does not require any particular knowledge.

CRIMINAL CASES 

209 

Com. v. Fairbanks, 2 Allen, 511

Opinion of a witness who is not an expert as to the sanity of one charged with a crime is incompetent. 

Washburn v. Cuddihy, 8 Gray, 430

Books on medical or veterinary practice cannot be read to the jury on an argument. 

Burt v. Burt, 168 Mass. 204

Whether a person is drunk is a question a person not an expert is competent to answer, as it is something which may fairly be considered to be a matter of common knowledge and observation. 

Res gestae. 

Com. v. Ratcliffe, 130 Mass. 36

If there is evidence that several persons are together for a common illegal entertainment, the declarations of one of them are admissible at the trial of an indictment against another one of them as a part of the res gestae. 

Benjamin v. McLellan, 237 Mass. 141

Conversation with the wife of the defendant before the assault is admissible to show provocation. 

Com. v. Roberts, 108 Mass. 296 

Three men indicted for stabbing a man. Immediately afterwards two of them assaulted another man, who cried out, "Look out; they've got a knife." Admissible. 

Com. v. Hackett, 2 Allen, 136 

Com. v. Smith, 2 Allen, 517

A declaration by the deceased immediately after the mortal blow that "A stabbed me" is admissible, although the defendant had run away before the declaration had been made. Threats uttered after commission of a crime are too late. Remoteness. 

Com. v. Quinn, 150 Mass. 401

Threats made three years previous are admissible. The defendant uttered threats that he would get even. Three years afterwards the defendant burned the barn of A and was caught at the fire by an officer who heard the defendant utter the threats. 

Not too remote. Admitted at the discretion of the court.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Crowe, 165 Mass. 139

Threats made in an arson case 14 month previous are admissible. 

Com. v. Retcovitz, 222 Mass. 245

Threats made 44 days prior to the commission of a murder by the defendant are admissible. 

Com. v. Ramey, 243 Mass. 394

At a trial on an indictment for assault with intent to kill evidence of an assault two years previous was admitted. The previous assault was by the same defendant on the same girl. 

Com. v. Cleary, 172 Mass. 175

A complaint was made to a mother the morning after a rape had been made on her daughter. The mother may testify to the same, not being too remote. 

Com. v. Lynch, 151 Mass. 358

Liquor was found on January 12. Evidence of pouring liquor into a sink on January 5. On January 5 seven men were seen leaving the rear of the house. January 7 seven men were found in the house of the defendant. No evidence of drunkenness. No evidence of a sale. Proper for the jury to consider in connection with the others. 

Com. v. Farmer, 218 Mass. 507

Representations made two years previous to the trial admitted to show intent. Threats made two years previous admissible. 

Judicial notice. 

Brodsky v. Fine, 263 Mass. 51

While courts will take judicial notice of the general laws and of public acts local in operation, they will not take cognizance of municipal ordinances or of special acts of the legislature. 

Boston v. Coon, 175 Mass. 283

A copy of the revised ordinances with the usual headings and attested at the end; the attestation covers the whole book and is admissible.

CRIMINAL CASES 

211 

Com. v. Ponzi, 256 Mass. 159

Courts recognize seals and signatures of heads of departments and principal officers of government, state and federal offices; applies to foreign papers. 

Com. v. Stevens, 196 Mass. 280

The law of another state must be proved as a fact. No presumption as to the law, and the putting in evidence of a volume does not put in evidence the marginal notes. See Chapter 233, Sections 69 and 70. 

Atty. Gen. v. Legion of Honor, 196 Mass. 151 

Where the common law of another state is material it is presumed in the absence of proof to the contrary to be the same as that of this state. See Chapter 233, General Laws. 

Bank v. Fenner, 192 Mass. 531

Where the law of another state becomes material, it is presumed in the absence of proof that it is the same in this state. 

See Chapter 233, Sections 69 and 70. 

Com. v. Inh. Springfield, 7 Mass. 9

The court will take judicial notice of towns created by law. 

Com. v. Peckham, 2 Gray, 514

The court will take judicial notice that distilled spirits are intoxicating. 

Com. v. Lanides, 239 Mass. 103

The court took judicial notice that jamaica ginger was intoxicating.

Testimony in the absence of the defendant, when admissible. 

Com. v. Keating, 133 Mass. 572

Two defendants arrested and charged with assault and battery. 

B had a conversation with the officer in the absence of A. Admissible as against B for the purpose of showing B's connection with the crime charged, even if in giving it, it affected A. 

Com. v. Bagdasarion, 257 Mass. 248

Conversation with an inmate of a house of ill fame as to bargaining in the absence of the defendant is admissible.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Sliney, 126 Mass. 49

Girl on the piazza of a house, the defendant twenty feet away; the girl held a conversation with a man regarding intercourse in the house; he spoke in a moderate voice, she in a loud voice. Question for the jury whether the defendant could have heard the conversation. 

Com. v. Sacco, 259 Mass. 128

The placing of spies in a cell adjoining that of the accused or placing them within the family is not an illegal method of obtaining evidence. 

Com. v. Colangelo, 256 Mass. 165 

Statements made by a girl that she, the girl, did not tell her mother are admissible. Testimony by the examining doctor of statements of the girl are admissible as proof that an assault had taken place, but not as proof that the defendant did it. 

Fay v. Harlan, 128 Mass. 244

A physician may testify to statements of pain and symptoms of the patient observed by him. 

Conduct of the defendant. 

Com. v. Borasky, 214 Mass. 313

The conduct of a defendant and his appearance after the crime has been committed are competent and bearing on the guilt of the defendant. 

Com. v. Goldberg, 212 Mass. 88 

Defendants may introduce evidence to show why they left town, not in fear of arrest; they may offer explanations. 

Com. v. Gangi, 243 Mass. 341

The conduct of the defendant when confronted with the complaint and the identification at the station house. The words of the complainant in identifying the defendant and the defendant's silence are admissible, who was not then under arrest. Defendant changed color. 

Com. v. Trefethern, 157 Mass. 180

Declarations showing the state of mind of the defendant are admissible. 

Com. v. Maderios, 255 Mass. 304 

Testimony that during confinement and before trial the defendant sawed the bars of his cell, assaulted the guard, and at-

CRIMINAL CASES 

213 tempted to escape is admissible as tending to show consciousness of guilt. 

Com. v. Brayman, 136 Mass. 438

Defendant charged with conveying real estate without making encumbrance known. Defendant could testify that at the time of the conveyance he, aged by years, had met with a great reverse of fortune, which so affected his mind that he had forgotten the encumbrance existed. 

Com. v. Brigham, 147 Mass. 414

A person attempting to escape from custody after being placed under arrest. It is a question for the jury as to how much weight this should be given in considering his guilt. 

Com. v. Tolliver, 119 Mass. 312

The fact that the defendant secreted himself immediately after the robbery is evidence of guilt. Also the fact that there was some blood on one of his bills alleged to have been stolen, the complainant having testified that she had bitten the finger of the assailant. It is a question for the jury. 

Com. v. Cooper, 219 Mass. 1

Mental responsibility of murder discussed. 

Com. v. Coyne, 228 Mass. 269

Possession of articles of value on the defendant, who does not work. Evidence is admissible. 

Com. v. Christie, 145 Mass. 232

Officers testified to certain conversations with the defendant, no others being present. This was contradicted by the defendant. 

The court charged the jury that material allegations may be proved beyond a reasonable doubt; that the jury was to consider the intelligence of the officer and whether he understood and interpreted the conversation correctly. 

Com. v. Cantor, 253 Mass. 509

Giving the accused the right to know from the arresting officer the grounds on which the arrest is made does not make the conversation competent at the trial. 

Telephone conversation - Voice. 

Com. v. Demboski, 283 Mass. 315

Evidence of a telephone conversation between a police officer and an unknown person to the effect that if he went to a

CRIMINAL CASES 

744 certain place at a certain time he would find the person that he had been looking for. Admissible only as to fixing the time. 

Com. v. Gettigan, 252 Mass. 450

It is not admissible unless the voice is recognized. 

Com. v. Harris, 232 Mass. 588

The testimony of a telephone operator that she received and noted a telephone message purporting to have come from a certain person is not admissible to show that such a message was sent by that person, where there is not any evidence as to the identity of the person who talked over the telephone to the operator. 

Com. v. Hayes, 138 Mass. 185

Testimony identifying a witness by his voice whom the witness had heard speak only once before the occasion of the commission of the crime charged is competent and may be considered by the jury in connection with other evidence of identity. 

Com. v. Williams, 105 Mass. 62

Witness can testify that he recognizes a person by his voice. 

It is for the jury in connection with other evidence how much reliance is to be put on it. Photographs. 

Com. v. Fielding, 184 Mass. 484

Photographs of evidence can be introduced as evidence. 

Com. v. Morgan, 159 Mass. 375 

Com. v. Hanks, 277 Mass. 268

May be introduced in evidence. They do not come within the prohibition by so doing one furnishes evidence against himself. 

Com. v. Johnson, 199 Mass. 55

In a prosecution for burglar's tools the photograph of the defendant can be introduced to the jury if there is not any description of the defendant thereon as to previous crimes. If there is, it is inadmissible. This applies to all cases as well as the above. 

Com. v. Retkovitz, 222 Mass. 245

Photographs of the deceased showing the brutality of the murder are admissible.

CRIMINAL CASES 

215 

Continuando. See also under Liquor and Nuisance. 

Com. v. Runge, 231 Mass. 598

Evidence of acts committed by the defendant before the time pleaded in the complaint is fatal. There must be a continuando complaint if there is evidence of more than one act. 

Com. v. Foley, 99 Mass. 497

Complaint alleged that on January 1 A was and is a common drunkard having been on divers times since January 1 drunk. 

Evidence is only admissible as of January 1. 

Com. v. Elwell, 1 Gray, 463

On the first day of January did presume to be a common seller of liquor. As this is a continuous offense evidence of sales must be confined to that day alone. 

Com. v. Briggs, 11 Met. 573

Where a complaint is pleaded continuando the evidence must be confined to acts done within that time. In other cases the date is not material, any time within the period of the statute of limitations except where time enters into the nature of the offense. Hearsay. 

Com. v. McKenna, 158 Mass. 207

On an appeal in a criminal case the witness cannot testify to what he heard another person who is ill and unable to attend the trial testified to in the lower court at the trial there. 

Com. v. Ricker, 131 Mass. 581

Indictment alleges the crime was committed on the 11th. The defendant offered evidence that up to the 28th he was out of the state. An officer was allowed to testify that an officer reported to him that he, the officer, saw the defendant on the 4th. Not admissible. 

Com. v. Wilson, 1 Gray, 337

Defendant charged with murder. Expressions of hostile feelings against the defendant by the deceased, not in the presence of the defendant and not within his knowledge, tending to show some real grounds for the defendant's feelings towards the deceased, are admissible.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Gallo, 275 Mass. 320

At the first trial of the defendant a woman gave testimony and was cross examined, and this was taken stenographically. 

On the second trial of the defendant it was admissible. She had been searched for, but the state was unable to find her. 

Circumstantial. 

Com. v. Murphy, 282 Mass. 593

The defendant was charged with uttering four checks and was identified by the receivers of the checks. The defendant offered evidence that at the time of the issuance of the checks he was in custody. It should have been admitted. Discussion of evidence of other crimes. 

Com. v. Snyder, 282 Mass. 401

At the trial of two defendants for murder, there was offered in evidence a statement made by one of them many months after the murder, which implicated the other one in the murder. The judge charged the jury that such statements were admitted only against the defendant who made them and not against the other defendant. Admissible. 

Com. v. Porfidio, 288 Mass. 61

A robbery was perpetrated and subsequently the defendant was arrested and a revolver was found in his possession on his person. The state need not show that the revolver found in his possession was the one used in the robbery; its possession in all the circumstances has some tendency to connect the defendant with the crime charged. 

Com. v. Mitchell, 248 Mass. 536

When guilt is sought to be established by circumstantial evidence, if one of the circumstantial facts essential to the chain of evidence is inconsistent with the guilt of the accused, the continuity of the proof is broken, and proof beyond a reasonable doubt by that set of circumstances is made impossible. 

Com. v. Leach,, 160 Mass. 542

In order to convict on circumstantial evidence it is not necessary to show that the crime could have been committed by someone other than the defendant, or that no person other than the defendant had the opportunity to commit it.

CRIMINAL CASES 

217 

Com. v. Cronan, 155 Mass. 393

A was standing in a crowd; he felt a tug at his watch chain; a little motion near his pocket; saw the chain drop, defendant's hand near the pocket go quickly down. Defendant was immediately arrested, no watch found. Enough evidence to convict. 

Com. v. Van Stone, 97 Mass. 548

Circumstantial evidence may well be founded on circumstances alone, provided the evidence of them excludes every other reasonable hypothesis but that of the guilt of the defendant. 

Secondary. 

Com. v. Carey, 2 Pick. 47

When it is impossible for the state to obtain the best evidence, the court must consider what is the next best evidence within its power to produce. 

Com. v. Emery, 2 Gray, 80

A registry copy of a deed of land is not admissible against the grantee without notice to him to produce the original. 

Com. v. Preece, 140 Mass. 276

When the original deed is not in the possession of the state or of the defendant so that it could not be produced on notice, a copy is competent. 

Exclusive opportunity. 

Com. v. Taylor, 210 Mass. 443

In order to prove by evidence wholly circumstantial, it is not necessary to prove defendant's exclusive opportunity. 

Com. v. Leach, 160 Mass. 542

In order to convict on certain evidence it is not necessary to show the crime could have been committed by someone other than the defendant or that no person had the opportunity to commit it. 

Com. v. Gentile, 255 Mass. 116

The state need not show nor prove that the defendant had the exclusive opportunity to commit the crime. Handwriting. 

Com. v. Allen, 128 Mass. 46

The court may properly exclude a paper writing offered by the defendant containing certain words written by him during

CRIMINAL CASES 

the trial for the purpose of being compared with the same words alleged to have been written by him at another time, the genuineness of which is in controversy. 

Com. v. Hunt, 4 Gray, 421

Witness testified that he had lost memorandum made in his writing and so testified. Later he wrote for the court, and another witness who originally appeared for the defendant testified that they were the same. Admissible. 

Com. v. Meehan, 170 Mass. 362

One witness was asked whether he knew the defendant's handwriting; his answer, he thought he did. Held that he could identify a name found on a paper in the defendant's possession. 

Foye v. Patch, 132 Mass. 105

One who cannot read or write can testify to the familiarity of handwriting of another. 

Com. v. Hall, 164 Mass. 152

One who is acquainted with the handwriting of another may testify concerning it. 

Violation of one crime affecting another.

Guinan v. Famous Players, 267 Mass. 516 

A violation of a statute, ordinance, or regulation, although not conclusive, is evidence of negligence on the part of the violator as to all consequences that the statute or ordinance or regulation was intended to prevent. 

Finnegan v. Skate Co., 189 Mass. 580 

Davis v. Whiting, 201 Mass. 91

While the violation of a statute or ordinance is not conclusive proof of negligence on the part of the violator, it may be held that the fact of the violation may be considered by the jury in passing on the question. 

Recent possession of property. 

Com. v. Montgomery, 11 Met. 534

Recent acquired money by one who was in destitute circumstances is to be considered by the jury. 

Com. v. Randall, 119 Mass. 107

A case of recent possession of property like in kind to that which has escaped from the hands of the true owner where no

CRIMINAL CASES 

219 sufficient explanation was given by the defendant establishes his guilt. 

Com. v. Crecorian, 264 Mass. 94

The defendant was indicted and tried for robbery. Evidence of an article stolen by the defendant belonging to one, other than the one he was being tried for, was admitted, it having been found on his person. There was no connection with the crime of robbery for which he was being tried and the larceny of the other article. 

Com. v. Millard, 1 Mass. 6

Parts of the stolen goods were found in the possession of the defendant. It is prima facie evidence that the defendant is guilty of the whole charge. 

Husband and wife. See also under same title. Marriage. 

Com. v. Norcross, 9 Mass. 492

The marriage of a party charged with adultery must be proven by witnesses to the ceremony. A copy of the record of the marriage is not required. 

Com. v. Littlejohn, 15 Mass. 163

Persons complained of for lewd cohabitation. The proof of the marriage of one of the defendants must be proved by the record of the clergyman or by witnesses present at the ceremony. Variance. 

Com. v. Goldsmith, 249 Mass. 159

A variance will not be considered when not claimed at the trial. 

Com. v. McCarthy, 145 Mass. 575

An allegation that the defendant threw a certain missile, to wit, a stone, at a railway car is not sustained by proof that he threw a billet of wood. 

Com. v. Teregno, 234 Mass. 56

If an indictment alleges that a knife was used, and the evidence shows that it was an ax, the defendant must be acquitted. 

Com. v. Leonard, 11 Gray, 458

It is not necessary to prove the offense charged in the complaint to the whole extent laid but that it is sufficient for the state to prove so much of the charge as constitutes a substantive offense therein specified.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. McLaughlin, 12 Cush. 615 

Where one is charged with assault- that is, discharging a pistol with intent to murder and from circumstances it appears that if death had ensued it would only have been manslaughter, he must be acquitted. Insanity. 

Com. v. Cooper, 219 Mass. 1 

Mental responsibility for murder discussed. 

Com. v. Pomeroy, 117 Mass. 143

When it is relied on in the defense of murder, evidence of the defendant's subsequent acts or conduct is not admissible to prove the existence of that condition at the time of the offense, excepting when it is so connected with evidence of the previous conduct, as to strengthen the inference of its continuance at the time of the murder. 

Com. v. Eddy, 7 Gray, 583

The burden is on the defendant to show that he is insane. Threats. 

Com. v. Costello, 119 Mass. 214

The defendant can introduce testimony that one of his witnesses has fled the state owing to threats of arrest by the state, he having made efforts to procure him for his defense. 

Com. v. Cosseboom, 155 Mass. 298

Threats by a person who is neither a witness nor a party to a criminal complaint are inadmissible to show prosecution was brought about by threats. 

Com. v. Ticinski, 189 Mass. 257

One on trial for manslaughter. Evidence of the general character of the deceased as a fighting man is admissible to show the defendant had reason to fear bodily harm. Self-defense. 

Com. v. Barnacle, 134 Mass. 215

On a trial for manslaughter in which the evidence shows the deceased made an attack on the defendant, on the issue whether the defendant acted in self-defense under a reasonable apprehension of bodily harm, evidence is admissible in his defense that

CRIMINAL CASES 

221 the deceased was a larger and more powerful man than the defendant. 

Com. v. Trippi, 268 Mass. 227

The right of self-defense does not accrue to a person until he has availed himself of all proper means in his power to decline the combat.

Joint defendants on being acquitted. 

Com. v. Griffin, 3 Cush. 523

In conspiracy, affray, riot, and perhaps some others where the agency of two or more is the essence of the offense, if one is acquitted, all must be. 

Name must be proved as laid. 

Com. v. Meehan, 11 Gray, 321

In a complaint for assault and battery, breaking and entering, larceny, etc., the name set forth must be proved as laid. But if the name differs only in spelling but has the same sound, whether they sound alike is for the jury, as: a sale of liquor to 

Hennessey is not supported by a sale to Heenessey if the jury finds that they do not sound alike. 

Idem sonans. See Pleading. 

Com. v. Donovan, 13 Allen, 571

Idem sonans is not a question of spelling but of pronunciation, and this is a question for the jury. 

Admissions to be taken and received with caution. 

Com. v. Sanborn, 116 Mass. 61 

Verbal admissions tending to show defendant making them was guilty of the crime should be received with great caution by the jury. 

Doctrine of entrapment. 

Com. v. De Lacey, 271 Mass. 327

An agent of the watch and ward society procured the sale of an obscene book from the defendant. The defendant was not an agent of the prosecutor and procurer, and the doctrine of entrapment does not apply as the defendant had previously committed the crime of selling the book.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Hospital record.

Clark v. Beacon Oil Co., 271 Mass. 27 

The words "odor of alcohol on breath" on a hospital record shall not be omitted in presenting the record as a whole in evidence. Cross-examination. 

Com. v. Lyden, 113 Mass. 452

The extent to which a witness can be cross-examined upon otherwise material facts for the purpose of showing bias or credibility is ordinarily a question for the court. 

Showing ownership. 

Com. v. Ayers, 115 Mass. 137

The complaint was for maintaining a common nuisance. The state can offer a conviction of keeping the place open on the Lord's day for the purpose of showing that the defendant kept the place.

Material fact. 

Com. v. Kennedy, 170 Mass. 18

A material fact must be proved by legitimate inference as well as by direct testimony.

Offering evidence after the state has rested. 

Com. v. Brown, 130 Mass. 279

At the trial of two defendants jointly, if one of them offers himself as a witness, his testimony is competent against the other defendant and may by permission of the court be introduced after the state has rested its case. 

Com. v. Blair, 126 Mass. 40

The state can introduce evidence after the defendant has finished putting in its evidence. It is a matter of judicial discretion and not the subject of exceptions. 

Common purpose. See Conspiracy. 

Com. v. Albert, 222 Mass. 196 

Com. v. Cline, 213 Mass. 225 

A complaint for attempting to commit larceny. Evidence of one defendant having an implement to cut open bags is admissible

CRIMINAL CASES 

223 as against all the defendants if acting for a common purpose. If indicted separately, they can be tried together. 

Conversation with the defendant. 

Com. v. Campbell, 155 Mass. 537

A conversation between the officer and the defendant which relates in part to the alleged crime and in part as to other matters is not admissible as to other matters but is admissible as to the crime. 

Records need not be produced, when. 

Com. v. Walker, 163 Mass. 226

The answers put to a prisoner in state prison in answers to questions of the warden can be testified to by the warden without the production of the records. 

Com. v. Dow, 217 Mass. 473

The official record of the fact made by a public officer in the performance of his duties may be introduced in evidence as to proof of the truth of the facts recorded. 

Acts of third persons. 

Com. v. N. Y. C. Ry., 202 Mass. 394

It is no defense to a complaint for obstructing the highway that the acts of a third person not in the employ of the railroad contributed and were the sole cause of the violation. 

Colorless evidence. 

Com. v. Williams, 171 Mass. 461 

Com. v. Mulrey, 170 Mass. 103

Evidence which is colorless in itself may be made significant by other evidence and so be made admissible. 

Parol proof. 

Com. v. Brown, 124 Mass. 318

One may testify as to placards and printed signs on a wall in describing a room. It is not showing by parol evidence the contents of a written instrument. 

Com. v. McGurty, 145 Mass. 257

Evidence which is destroyed by the state or its agents cannot be proved by secondary evidence (the description of ballots cast in an election does not fall within this rule).

CRIMINAL CASES 

1 

Com. v. Morrill, 99 Mass. 542

To identify the tag of a valise, oral evidence of words written on it is admissible without producing the tag or proving its loss. 

Com. v. Pope, 103 Mass. 440

Whenever evidence of the condition of clothes is competent and material, their condition may be described by witnesses without producing the articles themselves. 

Standing in one spot. 

Com. v. Jon Ri John Eliot, 121 Mass. 367

A complaint charged, "No person shall stand in a street to sell papers." If a person stands practically in the same location, moving backwards and forwards in front of a certain spot, he is guilty. If he moves along, occasionally stopping to sell papers and make change, he is not guilty. 

False statements on other occasions. 

Com. v. Kennon, 130 Mass. 39

At the trial of the defendant charged with an assault on a woman who testifies to the assault, evidence is competent for the purpose of proving a propensity to lie on the part of the woman, that she has lied on other occasions. 

Line-up of the defendant for identification. 

Com. v. Derby, 263 Mass. 39

One who picks out a man in the "line-up" and whose conversation with the officer is inaudible to the defendant can be asked on cross-examination as to that conversation. Letters. 

Com. v. Burns, 7 Allen, 540

A letter cannot be put in evidence for the purpose of showing when it was received by the person to whom it was addressed. 

Com. v. Edgerly, 10 Allen, 184

Inadmissible evidence such as letters cannot be read to the jury. 

Foreign language. 

Com. v. Shooshanian, 210 Mass. 123

A witness in a criminal case may be allowed to state in the English language the substance of the conversation in a foreign language.

CRIMINAL CASES 

225 

Testimony of a deceased person. 

Com. v. Glassman, 253 Mass. 65

The testimony of a deceased person in a subsequent issue can be testified to, provided the former testimony can be substantially reproduced in all the material particulars. 

Looking through glass. 

Com. v. Mullen, 97 Mass. 545

The witness saw evidence through glass in a door. Competent. 

Immaterial question. 

Alexander v. Kaiser, 149 Mass. 321

If a question is put to a witness on cross-examination which is collateral or immaterial to the issue, his answer cannot be contradicted. Interpreter. 

Com. v. Vose, 157 Mass. 393

Two persons each speaking a different language converse through a third person who understands only one language. The interpreter can testify for both the complainant and the defendant and his testimony affects the weight but not the competency of the evidence. 

On quashed counts. 

Com. v. White, 145 Mass. 392

The complaint was charged in 54 counts. The defendant moved and argued a motion to quash at the end of the trial, instead of at the beginning. All the evidence went in and 48 of the counts were quashed. The evidence on the ones that were quashed was admissible on the remaining six counts. Contradiction. 

Com. v. McIntosh, 259 Mass. 388

The defendant remained mute. Testimony can be introduced to contradict the witness and for that sole purpose only, but not so as to affect the defendant. The defendant had made a confession when arrested, he with two others.

When complainant receives a reward or part of the fine. 

Com. v. Pease, 137 Mass. 576

It is for the jury to consider evidence of an uncorroborated witness, when the witness is the complainant and receives one

CRIMINAL CASES 

half of the fine as a reward. The mere fact that his reward comes perhaps from his testimony does not render his evidence questionable. Motive. 

Com. v. Ramey, 243 Mass. 394

That a person charged with a crime had a motive for its commission is always competent. Erasures. 

Com. v. Fagan, 15 Gray, 194

In an indictment an interlineation affords no ground for arresting judgment. Bias. 

Com. v. Marcellino, 271 Mass. 325

On a complaint for assault and battery with intent to kill, a witness may be asked whether or not he has brought a civil suit against the defendant for the same cause. One can always show personal bias and interest in the outcome of litigation to affect credibility. 

Co-defendant-when he can testify. 

Com. v. Fuller, 260 Mass. 329

After a defendant has been eliminated by a plea of guilty or otherwise, he may be called to testify for the state. 

Capacity of a child to commit crime. See Juveniles and Rape. 

Com. v. Mead, 10 Allen, 396

If a child under the age of 12 years is charged with selling liquor in the presence of its mother and under her directions, the state must show a legal capacity to commit the crime. 

Com. v. Green, 2 Pick. 380 

A boy under the age of 14 years may be indicted for assault with intent to commit a rape.

Description of complainant, official designation. 

Com. v. O'Donnell, 1 Allen, 593 

A complaint which alleges one "H. F. Inspector of Milk" can only be regarded as "Descriptis Personae," and a plea of not guilty does not put his official character in evidence.

CRIMINAL CASES 

227 

Complicity of the principal. 

Com. v. Leavitt, 12 Allen, 179

If A hired men to drive cattle and they permitted them to go across a sidewalk in violation of an ordinance, he is guilty; if he only hired the men for somebody else and acted as an agent, only, he is not guilty.

Descriptive phrases. 

Com. v. Lewis, 1 Met. 151

In an indictment descriptive phrases can be rejected as surplusage. 

Admitting illegal evidence - Incrimination. 

Com. v. Kimball, 24 Pick. 366

If a witness refuses to testify on the grounds of incrimination and is by the court compelled so to do, and the defendant is convicted, the defendant may object that the conviction was founded on illegal evidence. 

Laws of other states. 

McRae v. Mattoon, 13 Pick. 53

Parol evidence is admissible to prove the unwritten law of another state, also the practice of the courts. 

Production of instrument under seal. 

Com. v. Phillips, 11 Pick. 22

A copy of the proceedings under seal duly certified is competent evidence of the existence of such record, in any other judicial tribunal of this state. 

Election fraud. 

Com. v. Bradford, 9 Met. 268

The state must prove in illegal voting not only that the defendant did not have the right to vote but that he knew it. 

Death of the witness. 

Com. v. Richards, 18 Pick. 434

If a witness dies who has testified previously, his testimony can be introduced at a subsequent trial, but it must be in toto.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Colorable sale. 

Com. v. Kimball, 24 Pick. 366

If property is transferred under a colorable sale, there is no real change. 

Aiding and abetting. 

Com. v. Barber, 261 Mass. 281

A complaint for assault and battery with intent to ravish. 

Persons present may be found guilty who were aiding and abetting in the actual offense, whether they committed the offense of assault or not.

EXAMINATION WAIVED 

Com. v. Goldstein, 180 Mass. 374

Examination waived from the lower court to the superior court has the same effect as a finding of probable cause. 

EXCEPTIONS 

Com. v. Flexner, 167 Mass. 13

Exceptions may be argued even after sentence has been passed and served. 

Allen v. Com., 255 Mass. 227

The defendant cannot file exceptions after the time of filing is passed, and the court is powerless to extend that time. 

EX POST FACTO 

Com. v. Phelps, 210 Mass. 78

Is a law that imposes punishment for an act which is not punishable at the time it was committed; or an additional punishment to that then enscribed; or changes the rule of evidence by which less or different testimony than then required is sufficient to convict; in short, in relation to the offense or its consequence alters the situation of a party to his disadvantage. 

Murphy v. Com., 172 Mass. 264 

Riley v. Com., 2 Pick. 165

As to the constitutionality of ex post facto laws.

229 

McDonald v. Com., 173 Mass. 322 

Com. v. Wyman, 12 Cush. 237

Discussion of ex post facto laws. 

Com. v. Homer, 153 Mass. 343

Evidence does not apply to an indictment found after the passage of the act for offense committed before its passage. 

EXTORTION GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 265, SEC. 25 AMENDED BY ACTS OF 1932, CHAP. 211 

Gist of the offense. 

Com. v. Corcoran, 252 Mass. 465

The attempt to extort money by threats is the gist of the offense under the statute; it is not dependent on the state of mind of the person threatened. Proof the defendant's exact words alleged is unnecessary. 

Com. v. Goodwin, 122 Mass. 19

The complaint charged that the defendant maliciously threatened, etc. The gist of the crime is to extort money; the words alone do not constitute the crime. In this case the extortion was to accuse of burning a building to defraud an insurance company; the pleader need not describe the building nor the amount of the insurance nor the name of the insuror.

Pleading. 

Com. v. Snow, 269 Mass. 598

The name of the person threatened must be specified in the indictment. It is not necessary to specify therein the name of the person against whom the injury is threatened. The wording of the statute may be followed and the person described as "another." An allegation threatening Nora Downes is good even if the name is Nora C. Downes. 

Com. v. Moulton, 108 Mass. 307

The word "thereby" is superfluous and need not be pleaded as it adds nothing to the meaning.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Robinson v. Com., 101 Mass. 27

In an indictment for extortion there must be an averment that the threats were made verbally or by a written or printed communication. 

Com. v. Pelligrini, 283 Mass. 300

An indictment charging that the defendant by a written or printed communication did maliciously threaten an injury to the person or property of another with intent thereby to extort money from a named person is sufficient, although the name of the individual whose property or person is threatened is not set out. 

The essential factors in such cases are: (1) malicious threats, (2) to extort money, (3) of personal injury to someone, (4) with intent to extort money. The statute requires simply that the person threatened be "another." In this case a letter in Italian. was left at a certain house where the person named lived. 

Com. v. Philpot, 130 Mass. 59

In a complaint for extortion, if the exact words are not set out but their substance is stated with sufficient certainty to comply with the provisions of the statute, it is sufficient. 

Evidence. 

Com. v. Corcoran, 252 Mass. 465

The person threatened need not be intimidated. The money need not have been paid. 

Com. v. Bagley, 7 Pick. 279

If an officer exacts the payment of a fee before it is due, this is extortion at common law. 

Com. v. Coney, 2 Mass. 523 

The receiving of a promissory note, not due, by an officer for fees will not support an indictment for extortion. If the officer had received money, an indictment would lie, but the note being given for an illegal consideration, an indictment could not be maintained. 

Com. v. Coolidge, 128 Mass. 55

The fact that A is owed a bill by B does not give the right to A to threaten to obtain it. 

Com. v. Pelletier, 264 Mass. 221

The threats relied on may be found in the words of the defendant, as contrasted with their acts, signs, looks, and conduct.

CRIMINAL CASES 

231 

Com. v. Bacon, 135 Mass. 521

In a trial of a case of threats the pleader need not mention that certain charges will be brought in court against the person threatened; but if they are such that they might reasonably be understood to embrace criminal prosecution, the jury might so find that they purported to do so. 

Com. v. Jones, 121 Mass. 57

Upon an indictment for extortion charging the defendant with maliciously threatening to accuse a person named therein for a crime with intent to extort money from him, evidence is admissible of the truth of the accusation upon the question of intent. 

Com. v. Carpenter, 108 Mass. 15 

Threatening to complain to an officer for a complaint against one for adultery and taking a promissory note to suppress it is extortion. The pleader need not set out the exact words if the purport is the same. 

Com. v. Murphy, 12 Allen, 449

A false statement that a warrant has been issued to arrest a person is a threat to accuse of a crime. A threat under the statute comprehends a threat to use any of the preliminary means to cause a person to be proceeded against for a criminal offense. 

Truth as a defense. 

Com. v. Jones, 121 Mass. 57 

Com. v. Buckley, 148 Mass. 27

Charges of truth will not constitute a defense. The malice required by statute is not a feeling of ill will towards the person threatened, but the willful doing of the act with illegal intent. 

If the threat made was willfully made with the intent to extort money, it was a malicious act, and the fact that the charge was true is immaterial. 

Other cases.

Carew v. Rutherford, 106 Mass. 1 

A conspiracy to injure a person's business by intimidating his help for the purpose of compelling him to pay sums of money discussed whether criminal or not at common law. 1

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Dorus, 108 Mass. 488

The word "seduce" in an extortion charge imports the commission of the crime of fornication or adultery. Need not set forth the words used in the threats. 

Com. v. Mosby, 163 Mass. 291

A threat made to arrest a person in a civil proceeding cannot be held to be a threat to injure the person of another within the meaning of the General Laws, Chapter 265, Section 25. This section means a threat to use actual physical force upon the person of another. This might be done under pretense of a fictitious legal process, or a process known to be void. It is not a threat to injure the person of another to charge that person with having committed a crime.

FALSE PRETENSES 

Com. v. Drew, 153 Mass. 588

One who purchases goods on credit to be sold in the ordinary course of trade but in fact disposes of them at one-half price is guilty of obtaining goods under false pretenses. It is a statement of fact and not a promise, and the underselling is not "dealing in the ordinary course of trade." 

Com. v. Wood, 142 Mass. 459

A statement that stock was selling for a certain price is indictable. Falsely representing that it was worth a certain price is not indictable. 

Com. v. Drew, 19 Pick. 179

A false pretense must relate to past events. Any representation or assurance in relation to a future transaction may be a promise or covenant or warranty but cannot amount to a statutory false pretense. The defendant presented a check to the bank and requested payment. If he had obtained the money under an assumed name and the bank had paid the money, on that assumption it would have been a false pretense. There must be a concurrence of four things, and four distinct averments must be proved. (1) There must be an intent to defraud. (2) There must be an actual fraud committed. (3) False pretenses must be used for the purpose of perpetrating the fraud. (4) The fraud must be accomplished by means of the false pretenses made use of for that purpose, they must be the cause which induced the owner to part with his property.

CRIMINAL CASES 

233 

Com. v. Althause, 207 Mass. 32

The fraud of obtaining goods by false pretenses by buying them with the intent not to pay for them might well be made a crime by the legislature, but it is not obtaining property by means of false pretenses. 

Com. v. Quinn, 222 Mass. 504

It has long been recognized that mere statements of the vendor concerning real or personal property where there is no warranty as to its value or the price which he has given or has been offered for it are to be treated as seller's talk and are not grounds for criminal action. If they are accompanied by deliberate affirmations having a tendency more effectually to deceive the purchaser, it is different. 

Com. v. Stevenson, 127 Mass. 446

The statement that one A was a merchant and had a place of business in Boston was a statement of a material fact. The statement that A was a man of wealth or worth a certain sum of money is a matter of opinion. 

Com. v. Este, 140 Mass. 279

If a treasurer of a town obtains money from a bank on a promissory note of the town and uses the money to pay town charges, he cannot be convicted of embezzlement although he does not account for it to the town, and although such use of the money is contrived as part of a scheme to defraud the town and to cover up an embezzlement already made and one intended to be made. 

Com. v. Langley, 169 Mass. 89

It is no defense on a complaint for false pretenses that property that came into the hands of the defendant passed into the hands of the corporation of which he was the treasurer. 

Com. v. Williams, 127 Mass. 285

In a complaint charging the defendant with conveying real estate without disclosing an encumbrance the allegation that the consideration was the payment of a sum of money is not supported by proof that the defendant, to secure release from arrest, gave a note secured by a mortgage. 

Com. v. Howe, 132 Mass. 250 

In all prosecutions for false pretenses the complaint must allege and the state must prove, first, that there was an actual

CRIMINAL CASES 

transaction, payment of money, or the delivery of property; second, that it was the defendant's purpose in making the false pretense to effect such transaction; third, that the party alleged to have been defrauded was actually deceived by the false pretenses. 

Com. v. Friedman, 188 Mass. 308

Under Section 30 of Chapter 266 of the General Laws the jurisdiction is any county where the defendant had possession of the goods, but under the section where the property was obtained by false pretenses it may be either where the pretenses were made or through which the property was carried or possessed. 

Com. v. Levenson, 250 Mass. 440

An attorney at law represented to his client that he, the defendant, required more money than that already paid, in order to make restitution. This statement was false. The court held that this statement was not a future promise or a future expectation but an assertion of a past or present fact. 

See also under Larceny.

FARE EVADING GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 266, SEC. 64 

Com. v. Jones, 174 Mass. 401

Passengers are bound by all reasonable rules, and one who refuses to obey one and leaves the car is guilty of evading a fare. 

Com. v. Northern St. Ry., 212 Mass. 82

The legislature has the right to fix fares for the transportation of school children, and the Acts of 1907, Chapter 567, is constitutional. 

Com. v. Goldman, 205 Mass. 400

An automobile is not a carriage under the evading fare statute. 

But now under the above chapter and section this has been amended and now is. 

Hull v. B. & M. Ry., 210 Mass. 159

The railroad police can arrest only for evading fare and for disorderly conduct on a train.

CRIMINAL CASES 

235 

FEES 

Sackett v. Sanborn, 205 Mass. 110

A chief of police in attending at a place other than his residence and not in the performance of his duties may be paid expenses. 

Reid v. Wright, 181 Mass. 306

Where a witness is expected to testify and goes to an office in close proximity to the court house, ready to go when called, and then is allowed to go for the rest of the day, he may fairly be said to have attended as a witness for that day, and a witness fee may be taxed. One need not attend all day to receive a fee. 

There is no such thing as a fraction of a day's attendance as a witness. 

Vogel v. Brown, 201 Mass. 261

It is illegal for a justice of the peace to charge more than $1.25 for performing a marriage, but he can demand and receive extra compensation if he goes to a residence and furnishes the couple whom he has married with a certificate. 

Com. v. Bundy, 5 Gray, 305

In a case where fees shall be taxed and allowed as part of the costs when a fine is imposed on appeal or acquittal, no fees are allowed. 

FINES 

Tyng v. City of Boston, 133 Mass. 272

The fire department has no power to punish a member of the department to "forfeit one month's pay," which amounted to $100, and the member can recover the same from the city. 

Com. v. Howard, 13 Mass. 221

Where the statute prescribes part of the fine to the commonwealth and part to the informer, the court decided that all should go to the commonwealth. 

Gabis Case, 240 Mass. 465

The defendant was sentenced to pay a fine of $100 and be committed for one month additional. Sentence was suspended. The defendant paid the fine and the execution of the sentence part was suspended. Subsequently he was surrendered and the sentence

CRIMINAL CASES 

was imposed. The court can suspend part of a sentence.

See 

General Laws, Chapter 279, Section 1A. 

Harris v. Com., 23 Pick. 280

Where a statute authorizes a punishment by fine, cost may be awarded as incident and the party convicted may be committed till such costs and fine be paid although costs are not authorized in terms in the statute. 

Wilde v. Com., 2 Met. 408

A sentence to imprisonment if a fine and costs be not paid within a limited time is not for nonpayment of the fine and costs, but for the offense for which the defendant is convicted; and if the fine and costs be not paid within the time limited, the sentence to imprisonment remains in force, although for a longer time than that in which the defendant would be released from the fine by operation of law. 

Gannon v. Adams, 8 Gray, 395

A defendant sentenced to imprisonment and fine and costs for being a common seller of liquor is not entitled to be discharged as a poor convict until three months from the expiration of the time for which he was sentenced to be imprisoned. 

Bean v. Crosby, 1 Allen, 220

The nonrecital in the mittimus that the complaint on which the defendant is committed omits that the complaint was under oath or to whom the fine was to be paid and the designation of the jail to which he was to be committed, is immaterial. 

Bills v. Comstock, 12 Met. 468 

Kingsbury v. Ellis, 4 Cush. 578 

A promissory note given by the defendant for nonpayment of fine to either a committing officer or the justice is void. 

FIRE PREVENTION GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 148 

Com. v. Badger, 243 Mass. 137

Under Section 36 as to the notice to the owner of the building the notice must be explicit. 

Com. v. Kidder, 107 Mass. 188

The emission of vapors where petroleum is manufactured is a nuisance at common law.

CRIMINAL CASES 

237 

Com. v. Parks, 155 Mass. 531

A city ordinance prohibiting the blasting of rocks with gunpowder within the city limits without the consent of the board of aldermen is constitutional. 

Flynn v. Butler, 189 Mass. 377

A magazine for the storage of gunpowder in a populous neighborhood is a nuisance at common law. 

Com. v. Packard, 185 Mass. 64

The defendant was charged continuando with storing large quantities of naphtha on his premises kept and maintained by him for the purpose, near private houses and public highways by reason thereof great quantities of offensive, noxious, and unwholesome smells were emitted and impregnated the air, making it unhealthy; a nuisance at common law. 

Com. v. Wentworth, 118 Mass. 441

Guilty knowledge is not one of the ingredients in the offense imposing a penalty upon a person who shall sell or keep or offer for sale naphtha under any assumed name. The fact that the defendant mixed chemicals with the naphtha which destroyed its explosive qualities did not materially change this, as the court charged the jury whether the chemical mixed substantially changed the naphtha or not. 

Com. v. Murphy, 166 Mass. 171

Carrying firearms in a parade is a violation even if they cannot be discharged. Acts 1893, Chapter 367, Section 123, is constitutional.

FISH AND GAME 

Com. v. Sisson, 189 Mass. 247

The legislature may delegate to the fish and game commissioners, and the commissioners can act in a legislative capacity. 

Com. v. Gilbert, 160 Mass. 157

The penalty for selling trout extends to the sale of trout artificially propagated and maintained. 

Com. v. Tiffany, 119 Mass. 300

A pond in which fish are artificially propagated, cultivated, and maintained cannot be fished in without the permission of the owner.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Howes, 270 Mass. 69

One who has a fee in lands cannot take shellfish from it without a license, if the area from which they were taken is within the prohibited areas. 

Com. v. Weatherhead, 110 Mass. 175

The commonwealth can lease part of a great pond to individuals for the cultivation of fish, and can bar any person other than themselves from fishing in it. 

Com. v. Hall, 128 Mass. 410

A statute providing that whoever has in his possession in this state or kills between certain days certain birds, is not punishable for having in his possession birds killed in another state. 

Dunn v. Lowe, 203 Mass. 516

A search of a sack of lobsters being carried in the hands is not authorized. See General Laws, Chapter 130, Sections 102 and 112. 

Averill v. Chadwick, 153 Mass. 171

A deputy of the game commissioner without any order of the court seizes game unlawfully exposed for sale, is guilty of conversion. The commissioner cannot authorize the seizure. 

FORFEITURE 

See also under Alcoholic beverages. 

Savings Bank v. Searles, 237 Mass. 489

Where an officer of the law or another person in the exercise of a power conferred by law rightfully takes property but afterwards fails to comply with the provisions of the law in disposing of it, becomes a trespasser ab initio and fails in his justification. 

A police officer took a revolver from a prisoner and did not return the same on demand. 

Russell v. Hanscomb, 15 Gray, 166

A fishwarden who takes a net which is unlawfully set across a river and keeps it for 14 days without instituting legal proceedings as required by law becomes a trespasser ab initio and is liable to the owner.
