239 

Pleading. 

FORGERY GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 267 

Com. v. Clancy, 7 Allen, 537

In setting out "passing as true an uncurrent bank bill" the omission in the indictment of the cashier's and president's name is fatal. 

Com. v. Hall, 97 Mass. 570 

Uttering a counterfeit bank bill-form of complaint. 

Com. v. Wilson, 2 Gray, 70

It is a fatal variance in a complaint for uttering a forgery to omit the name of the state in the upper margin of the bill, if not repeated in the body thereof. The words "State of Maine" are part of the date and so part of the contract. 

Com. v. Taylor, 5 Cush. 605

The number and check letter of a forged instrument and the words in the margin need not be set forth in the indictment. 

Com. v. Houghton, 8 Mass, 107

The pleader must describe the counterfeit bill or set forth the reason for not so doing. 

Com. v. Atwood, 11 Mass. 93

Words in an indictment which may have been the means of a verdict cannot be rejected as surplusage to support a conviction. 

Com. v. Ladd, 15 Mass. 526

In an indictment it is not necessary to allege that the goods were delivered in accordance with an acquittance; the false making with intent to defraud is the gist of the offense. 

Com. v. Stevens, 1 Mass. 203 

Com. v. Bailey, 1 Mass. 62

The word "tenor" binds to a strict recital, not the number of the bankbill, and the words at the top, figures in the margin, expressing the amount, are no part of the bill and need not be set out in the indictment; only the evidence of the contract need be alleged. 

Com. v. Snow, 1 Mass. 53

Alleging the instrument to be "in the words and figures following" a strict recital is necessary.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Ross, 2 Mass. 373

An indictment for uttering a promissory note need not be set forth with the date of the note nor the time when the money is made payable. 

Com. v. Goldsmith, 249 Mass. 159

A description of and what a stock certificate is. 

Com. v. Dunleavey, 157 Mass. 386 

A complaint must aver a false making for the purpose of fraud, also that the forged instrument is of such a description that it might defraud or deceive if used with that intent. If it does not appear on the surface, the pleader must aver matter as would enable the court judicially to see the tendency. 

Com. v. Spillman, 124 Mass. 327

In pleading it is necessary that words alleged to be forged be shown or averred to have such a relation to the contract as to be the subject of forgery. It is necessary to show affirmatively that the words written become a part of the instrument which is the subject of forgery. 

Com. v. Hinds, 101 Mass. 209

In pleading forgery at common law the fraud must be particularly pleaded to show that a crime has been committed. The instrument itself does not show on its face that it is a forgery. 

Com. v. Ward, 2 Mass. 397

An indictment for forging a note need not allege the endorsement of the note though it be forged. It is no part of the note. 

Com. v. Bailey, 199 Mass. 583

A description of the instrument need not be set out. A certain forged instrument purporting to be a promissory note is sufficient. Not considered, though, to be the best pleading. 

Com. v. Parsons, 138 Mass. 189

A forgery of a check is properly pleaded as an order for money. 

Meserve v. Com., 137 Mass. 109

A discharge of a mortgage is a deed in so far as relates to a forgery of a deed. A certificate of a justice of the peace may be received as a legal proof in charging a forgery. 

Com. v. Lawless, 101 Mass. 32 "Received of A. B., Surgeon General, my discharge and check for $100" is not an accountable receipt and is bad pleading. It

CRIMINAL CASES 

241 does not acknowledge that anything has been received which is to be accounted for. 

Com. v. Adams, 7 Met. 50

The endorsement of the payee is not a part of the note, merely evidence of a transfer and it is not necessary to set forth the manner in which the party was intended to be defrauded. 

Evidence. 

Com. v. Dallinger, 118 Mass. 439

An instrument purporting to be signed by J. S. payable to the order of J. S. is not a promissory note until endorsed. 

Com. v. Henry, 118 Mass. 460

There must be an intent to defraud. The state need not prove that anyone was actually defrauded. The defendant's intention to take up a forged note at maturity does not deprive the transaction of the criminal character. 

Com. v. Talbot, 2 Allen, 161

A receipt in full for all demands is a discharge for money. The possession of a forged instrument by the person claiming under it is strong evidence tending to prove he forged it or caused it to be forged. 

Com. v. Ayer, 3 Cush. 150

The common law relating to forgery is not superseded by the statute, and any forgery which is not enumerated in the statute is still indictable at common law. 

Com. v. Peck, 1 Met. 428

The party whose signature is alleged to have been forged is a competent witness to prove the forgery. 

Com. v. Carey, 2 Pick. 47

The proof of the signature by the person can be testified to although he had never seen the president or the cashier write. 

Com. v. Hill, 11 Mass. 136 

One who induced an ignorant boy to utter a forged note may be convicted of the uttering; it is a sufficient passing. 

Com. v. Clune, 162 Mass. 206 

A prepared a forged check and delivered it to B. B got a messenger to cash it. A is guilty of uttering.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Boynton, 2 Mass. 77

Uttering a bank bill with a fictitious name of a cashier countersigned thereon is not an indictable offense unless uttered with an intent to defraud, and it is a misdemeanor at common law. 

Com. v. White, 145 Mass. 392

A receipted bill for goods is an acquittance and discharge for money. 

Com. v. Hayward, 10 Mass. 34

The altering of, or the cutting out of a piece of, a genuine note to make an additional one is not an indictable offense; it is not within the provision of law against altering bills, which means alteration that will increase the apparent value of the bill. 

Com. v. Welch, 148 Mass. 296

An endorsement on the note of a stranger to the note does not change the legal effect of it. 

Com. v. Snell, 3 Mass. 82

Where the alleged instrument has been secreted the person whose name has been forged and who has seen the instrument is a competent witness.

Finnegan v. Lucey, 157 Mass. 439 

Signing does not necessarily mean a written signature as distinguished from a signature by mark, by print, by stamp, or by the hand of another. It is enough if her name is signed by her in her presence and at her request, she knowing and understanding the contents of the instrument. 

Com. v. Russell, 156 Mass. 196

In prosecutions for uttering forged paper it is admissible in order to show intent to defraud by forgery, and also to show knowledge on the part of the accused with reference to the principal document which the defendant is charged with uttering, that at or near the time of committing the crime he had passed or had in his possession other similar forged documents.

Bishops Criminal Law, Vol. 2-586 

Forgeries of entries in a book of account. The making of an original false record is not forgery, but there must be a fraudulent alteration or that it shall appear not to be the writing which it is designed to be.

CRIMINAL CASES 

243 

Com. v. Boutwell, 129 Mass. 124

A fraudulent insertion of additional words or an alteration in a material part of a document by which another may be defrauded is a forgery. 

Com. v. Foster, 114 Mass. 311 

One who with intent fraudulently to utter a promissory note as a note of a person other than the signer procures to it the signature of an innocent who does not thereby intend to bind himself is guilty of a forgery. 

Com. v. Buttrick, 100 Mass. 12

An averment that the whole instrument was forged is satisfied by proof of a forgery of any material part thereof. 

Putnam v. Sullivan, 4 Mass. 45

If one entrusts his clerk with blank endorsements and another person obtains one by fraud and uses it, this does not constitute forgery. 

Com. v. Parmenter, 5 Pick. 279

The fact of forging a note within a county cannot be inferred from its having been uttered there. 

Com. v. Whitney, Thatcher's Crim. Cases, 588

If one signs the name of another to an instrument with the honest belief that he has the authority to do so, it negatives the intent to defraud.

Com. v. Chandler, Thatcher's Crim. Cases 

The execution of an instrument in the name of a fictitious person or under an assumed name with intent to defraud is forgery. 

Com. v. Baldwin, 11 Gray, 197 

Signing a promissory note in the name of a fictitious firm with intent to defraud and falsely representing that the firm consists of the writer and another person is not forgery. 

State v. Hayden, 15 N. H. 355 

The forgery of a fictitious name is indictable. 

Com. v. Ray, 3 Gray, 441 

Not a felony at common law. Counterfeiting a railroad ticket is a forgery at common law.

CRIMINAL CASES FUGITIVE FROM JUSTICE GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 276, SECS. 11–20 Henry's Case, 29 How. Pr. 185 

State v. Anderson, 1 Hill (S. C.) 212

One can be arrested on a telegram from the demanding state. 

There is not any case on this subject in this state but in connec tion see Scott v. Eldredge, 154 Mass. 25. 

Scott v. Eldredge, 154 Mass. 25 

The defendant was arrested on the receipt of a letter from the State of Pennsylvania requesting the arrest. No indictment was sent. If the crime was a felony in the demanding state, the defendant could be arrested here without a warrant. In this case the crime was not a felony, hence the arrest here was illegal. 

1 Sandford (N. Y.) 701

The crime in a complaint for a fugitive from justice must be set out with clearness as to the facts. See Vorhees on arrest, page 232. 

Bricknall v. Speigel, 68 Conn. 441 - - The plaintiff was tried and sentenced in the State of New York for burglary. During his term of imprisonment he was permitted to leave the Reformatory upon his agreement to comply with certain conditions sanctioned by the State of New York. 

These conditions he violated when he fled to the State of Connecticut, where he was arrested in response to a request for his surrender to the New York Authorities. Held that inasmuch as the responsibility for the crime of burglary continued until the term of his imprisonment was satisfied, he was still charged with the crime within the meaning of the expression used in Section 2 of the Federal Constitution relative to extradition. That he was none the less a fugitive from justice because he had escaped from imprisonment after being allowed to go outside the prison walls on his parole.

Opinion of Justices, 201 Mass. 609 

A person who is serving a sentence in this state cannot be delivered to another state. 

Graves Case, 236 Mass. 493

In granting extradition the Governor can go outside the evidence presented in the papers.

CRIMINAL CASES 

245 

Com. v. Wright, 158 Mass. 149

A fugitive from justice who has been surrendered by another state may be tried here for an offense other than that specified in the requisition, although committed before the fugitive was demanded without giving him the opportunity to return to the state from which he was extradited. 

Com. v. Rice, 130 Mass. 231

A fugitive from justice whose domicile was in this state at the time of flight and who has acquired no domicile elsewhere may be proceeded against here in the bankruptcy court after his flight. 

Davis Case, 122 Mass. 324

The court cannot discharge the defendant on a writ of habeas corpus because of formal defects in the indictment. Its sufficiency is a matter of pleading to be tried and determined in the state in which it is first found. 

Brown's Case, 112 Mass. 409

The question is not whether the statement in the warrant would be sufficient in an indictment to put the prisoner on trial in the fleeing state, but whether it shows that he has been charged with a crime in the demanding state. 

Wallace, Petitioner, 265 Mass. 101

At a hearing before the governor strict compliance with the common law rule of evidence is not required. Where there is contradictory evidence as to the presence of the defendant in the demanding state, the governor will honor the demand. A writ of habeas corpus is not the proper proceeding to try questions of alibi or any question as to the innocence or guilt of the accused. 

Formal objections to the complaint are for the courts of the demanding state and not for the courts of this state on a habeas corpus petition. 

Germaine, Petitioner, 258 Mass. 289

When it is conceded or when it is conclusively proved that no question can be made that the person was not within the demanding state when the crime is said to have been committed, and his arrest is sought on the ground only of a constructive presence at that time in the demanding state, then the court will discharge the defendant. Whether the person demanded is a fugitive is a question of fact to be decided by the governor of the asylum state.

CRIMINAL CASES Kingsbury Case, 106 Mass. 223 

One who lives in Massachusetts and goes to Maine and there commits a crime and returns to Massachusetts is a fugitive from justice from Maine. The demand shall be accompanied by sworn evidence that the party is a fugitive, a duly attested copy of the indictment or a duly attested copy of the complaint and supported by affidavits. It is not necessary to inquire into the question of guilt. 

Com. v. Hall, 9 Gray, 262

The governor of Massachusetts issued a warrant for the arrest of a fugitive from the demanding state, and a similar warrant to the sheriff of the commonwealth reciting the facts that the other process had been issued. Held that an arrest by a deputy sheriff under the second warrant and the discharge of the alleged fugitive on bail under a writ of habeas corpus did not prevent an arrest by the agent of the demanding state under the first warrant. 

Thompson v. Boston Globe, 279 Mass. 176

Under Section 33 of Chapter 218 of the General Laws clerks of district courts can issue process relative to rendition. Whether or not there was a crime under the laws of the demanding state is immaterial. 

Ex Parte Wallace, 265 Mass. 101 

Request for ruling that governor could not issue warrant for arrest because demand was not accompanied by sworn evidence that the petitioner was a fugitive properly refused. Gen eral Laws, Chapter 276, Section 11. 

GAMING GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 271 

Lottery. See under same heading.

Gaming implements. 

Com. v. Adams, 160 Mass. 310

A gaming implement is any article, utensil, or appliance used in playing an unlawful game, although not indispensable. It does not matter if the article is susceptible of a lawful use.

CRIMINAL CASES 

247 

Com. v. Gam. Imp., 155 Mass. 165

Implements seized require that in the absence of a finding that they are not unlawfully used or intended to be used they shall be delivered to the owner. 

Com. v. Gam. Imp., 141 Mass. 114

It is too late to introduce evidence in the Superior Court of a faulty return of an order of notice on an appeal from the forfeiture of gaming implements. 

Com. v. Dana, 2 Met. 329

Books kept in relation to a lottery are materials for a lottery. 

Coolidge v. Choate, 11 Met. 79 

Game cocks are not implements of gaming and cannot be lawfully seized on a search warrant for gaming implements. "Implements" and "apparatus" have the same meaning. See

Chapter 272, Sections 88-91. 

Bet -Wager Hazard. 

99 Maine, 486

A bet is a hazard of money or property on an incident by which one or both parties stand to win or lose by chance. 

Com. v. Watson, 154 Mass. 135

Selecting a number and playing for it is a wager and pool. 

Jones v. Cavanaugh, 149 Mass. 124 

A foot race between two people is gaming. 

Murphy v. Rogers, 151 Mass. 118

Playing billiards on terms that the loser shall pay for the table liquor and cigars for the winner is gaming. 

Goldberg v. Feiga, 170 Mass. 146

The act relative to gaming does not apply to a mere stakeholder who pays over the money in accordance with the terms on which he received it, and before he receives any notice that the authority given to him to pay it over has been revoked. 

Fisher v. Hildredth, 117 Mass. 558 

Ball v. Gilbert, 12 Met. 397

A wager on an election is illegal.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Being present when implements of gaming were found. 

Com. v. Smith, 166 Mass. 370

Under a complaint for being present when gaming implements were found the pleader need not aver that the defendant had knowledge of the presence of the implements, or character of the place where they were found. The officer can take the ticket from the person of the defendant at the station house. Persons who resort to a gaming house do so at their own peril. 

Com. v. Warren, 161 Mass. 281

The word "place" in a search warrant means building or house, not confined to the room where such persons were found when arrested. Gaming implements were found on the stairway leading up to the place where the implements were found. The state need not prove by direct evidence that on numerous occasions persons resorted to the house for that purpose. Evidence may be circumstantial or the facts disclosed may be sufficient to indicate that the place was once used as a place of resort for the purpose named. 

Com. v. Hogarty, 141 Mass. 106

One complained of for being present when gaming implements were found can be convicted if the evidence tends to show that he was actually playing. 

Registering bets and selling pools. 

Com. v. Sullivan, 218 Mass. 281

Keeping a room for the purpose of registering bets and selling pools is made out, if the room is shown to have been kept for either purpose. Selling coupon books on baseball comes within the above. 

Com. v. Healey, 157 Mass. 455

Six men complained of for being present. A blackboard and telegraph instrument were in the room. The defendants did different work but can be jointly held. The board and instruments were capable of other uses but were on the premises as aids in the registering of bets. 

Com. v. Clancy, 154 Mass. 128

The state need not in a complaint for registering bets prove that the defendant registered bets and sold pools. If he does either he is guilty. If the business embraces more than one it is still an offense.

CRIMINAL CASES 

249 

Com. v. Watson, 154 Mass. 135 

Issuing a memorandum of a sale is registering a bet. Paying money to A in a pool room after selecting a number would be selling a pool even if no words were spoken. Not necessary that it should embrace both. It is within the statute if more than one. May still be one business and one offense. 

Com. v. Moody, 143 Mass. 177

The offense of occupying a room in a building with books, apparatus, etc., to sell pools is a single averment and is not bad for duplicity. 

Com. v. Ferry, 146 Mass. 203

The complaint need not allege any detailed description of the apparatus or of the particular contests. The complaint charged the date of the offense as of May 28. Evidence is admissible that ten days previous the defendant occupied the same rooms on the issue whether it was so kept on the 28th. 

Com. v. Swain, 160 Mass. 354

In a form for occupying a room with apparatus, etc., by unknown persons, the pleader need not aver them, only the names of the men who are complained of for being present in the said rooms. 

Sullivan v. Vorenberg, 241 Mass. 319

The receiving of a bet on a horse race and the making of a memorandum there on a slip of paper, delivered to the bettor, is the registering of a bet and is illegal. 

Winning more than $5 at one sitting. 

Com. v. Novak, 272 Mass. 113

The fact that the penalty prescribes a forfeiture does not take it out of the statute. A forfeiture is the penalty or fine imposed for the offense. 

Plummer v. Gray, 8 Gray, 243

Money lost, to be recovered cannot be maintained by the loser after the expiration of three months from the loss. 

What is gaming? 

Com. v. Gourdier, 14 Gray, 390

Com. v. Theatre Adv. Co., 286 Mass. 405

CRIMINAL CASES 

Skill and chance.

The complaint was for a game called beano, keeping a nuisance. Gaming for money or other property is illegal. The judge charged the jury, "If you find that the money he obtains or the prize he obtains is induced in any way by the playing of that game, either by chance or skill, then you can come to the conclusion that that constitutes illegal gaming under our laws." 

Garvey v. McNulty, 270 Mass. 260

Playing poker with chips for counters affords a presumption at least, and it may be prima facie evidence at least that the chips represented something of value, that the game was played for money or something of value. 

Com. v. Taylor, 14 Gray, 26 

To play any game or hazard for money or other article of value, a game of hazard to determine who shall pay for the drinks to be drunk is strictly playing for money. It is to determine which party shall pay a sum of money to the other (the game was a game of hustling cents). 

Common gaming house. See also under Nuisance. 

Com. v. Blankenship, 165 Mass. 40 

Com. v. Chung, 193 Mass. 383

To make a building a common gaming house it is not necessary that it should be open to all the public. 

Com. v. Warren, 161 Mass. 281 

Com. v. Ward, 281 Mass. 119

The word "place" as used in being found present refers to the house or building and is not confined to the room where such persons were found when arrested. A thick barricaded door, a peek hole through which the keeper looked, a partition where the implements were concealed. 

Com. v. Adams, 160 Mass. 310

A room in which a blackboard devoted to policy, in which all business stops on a visit of an officer, may well be found to constitute a nuisance. 

Trading stamp law. 

Com. v. Sessions, 178 Mass. 578 

Com. v. Emerson, 165 Mass. 146

The trading stamp law is not gaming and is constitutional. 

Acts of 1898, Chapter 576.

CRIMINAL CASES 

251 

Opinion of Justices, 208 Mass. 607 

O'Keeffe v. City of Somerville, 190 Mass. 110

General Laws, Chapter 271, Section 29, does not prohibit the giving of trading stamps or coupons with a purchase. As long as the purchaser is not induced to purchase any article on the promise that he will obtain something in the gambling sense, the giving of stamps and coupons is legitimate. 

Letting premises, etc. See under Nuisance. 

Com. v. Dean, 18 Mass. 386

The defendant let a shed adjoining the defendant's store on an alleyway which was used for gaming. The defendant did not actually occupy it and could not be held. He was complained for occupying, which was untrue. 

Horse racing. 

Com. v. Rosenthal, 195 Mass. 116

General Laws, Chapter 271, Section 31, does not legalize betting at trials of horses for speed for premiums offered by an agricultural society. It merely provides that racing for such premiums shall be allowed. 

What is gambling? 

Buckley v. O'Neil, 113 Mass. 193 

To gamble is to play or game for money. 

Cock fighting. 

Com. v. Tilton, 8 Met. 232 

Cock fighting is an unlawful game or sport. "Game" and "sport" are synonymous. An innholder who used his house to exercise cock fighting is guilty. 

Suffering persons, etc. 

Com. v. Stowell, 9 Met. 572

Suffering persons to resort to a place for the purpose of gaming is not a continuing offense. The pleader need not allege that persons resorted there or did play. 

Recovery of prize money by attorney general. 

Com. v. Howes, 15 Pick. 231

Procedure under Section 14 of Chapter 271 as to recovering prizes and money obtained by lottery.

CRIMINAL CASES Illegal consideration. 

Jones v. Cavanaugh, 149 Mass. 124 

Fisher v. Hildredth, 117 Mass. 558

In order for the plaintiff to recover he must show that he bet his money on the foot race, that he lost the bet, and the defendant or his agent was the winner, in order to recover outside the statute, and that the plaintiff must make a demand before bringing suit. 

Com. v. Groves, 134 Mass. 471

To recover money lost under the statute, if the action is not actually brought within the statute of limitations, no action will lie. 

Morgan v. Beaumont, 121 Mass. 7

A party to an illegal wager who demands his money from a stakeholder before it is paid over is not in pari delecto, and can recover his deposit from him; and the fact that the defendant knew of and promoted the wager affords him no protection. 

White v. Buss, 3 Cush. 448

Two persons playing; one borrows money from the proprietor to continue playing; it is illegal consideration and cannot be recovered back. Any promise or contract which would tend to promote, advance, or carry into effect an object or purpose which is unlawful is in itself void, and will not maintain an action. 

Babcock v. Thompson, 3 Pick. 446 

Ball v. Gilbert, 12 Met. 397 

All gaming is unlawful, and money lost by fair or foul play cannot be recovered back by the loser nor his executor or administrator in an action begun three months from the losing. 

GAMING SEARCH WARRANTS GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 271, SECS. 5 AND 23 GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 276, FOR PROCEDURE 

Atty. Gen. v. Gam. Imp., 103 Mass. 456

Under this decision money was not the subject of forfeiture but under the Acts of 1934, Chapter 235, this has now been changed and is the subject of forfeiture.

CRIMINAL CASES 

253 

Com. v. Gam. Imp., 119 Mass. 332

Under a warrant an officer can seize all the furniture and personal property in the rooms described. In the return on the order of notice the fact that the order of notice was posted on the building is notice to all concerned if the return on it shows that search was made by the officer for the owner. 

Com. v. Blankenship, 105 Mass. 40

Rooms used for gaming by a club and such persons as the members invite constitute a common gaming house. 

Com. v. Dana, 43 Mass. 329

A search warrant for a lottery ticket is constitutional. 

Atty. Gen. v. Gam. Imp., 103 Mass. 456

Where gaming implements are seized together with furniture and money without arresting anyone on the warrant for being present or gaming, the court cannot make an order to dispose of them without notice to the owner. 

Com. v. Lottery Tickets, 5 Cush. 369

On a warrant for lottery tickets the complainant must take oath to his belief to the facts stated in the complaint. It is not sufficient for him to swear that he has reasonable cause to suspect and does suspect that the facts therein stated are true. Tickets seized under a warrant illegally issued are not liable to be burned or destroyed.

Coolidge v. Choate, 11 Met. 79 

Game cocks are not gaming implements and cannot be lawfully seized on a search warrant for gaming. Apparatus and implements are and have the same meaning. 

GRAND JURY Opinion of Justices, 232 Mass. 601 

The presence of police officers in a grand jury room while witnesses are testifying is unconstitutional. 

Com. v. Harris, 231 Mass. 584

Two witnesses and a police officer were in the grand jury room while the testimony was being given. Unconstitutional.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Lebowich, 235 Mass. 357

The presence of an interpreter or an official stenographer is legal. Discussion as to who may be in the room at the investigation. 

Crimm v. Com., 119 Mass. 326 

Com. v. Wood, 2 Cush. 149

A grand jury may consist of not less than thirteen and no more than twenty-three men. 

Com. v. McNary, 246 Mass. 46

A grand jury in some cases may hear witnesses on behalf of the defendant. 

Com. v. Sanborn, 116 Mass. 61

It is not essential to the validity of an indictment that the grand jurors should have been instructed by the justice. 

Com. v. Smythe, 11 Cush. 473

The omission of the words "true bill" does not vitiate the indictment.

HABEAS CORPUS GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 248 Sellors Case, 186 Mass. 301 

If one has been sentenced for a misdemeanor upon a default, a writ will not lie. The remedy is a writ of error. 

Dowdell, Petitioner, 169 Mass. 387

An insane person committed under the statute is not entitled to a writ. The restraint is beneficial and the commitment is constitutional.

Graves Case, 236 Mass. 493 

On a writ the defendant is not entitled to a trial by jury of the facts placed in issue by the defendant. Fugitive from justice. 

Com. v. Huntley, 156 Mass. 236

The defendant was sent to jail for the nonpayment of a fine. 

A writ was issued to bring the defendant before the superior court for the arguing of the exceptions. This is not to be taken as a precedent.

CRIMINAL CASES 

255 

Sturtevant v. Com., 158 Mass. 598

The complaint was for being a habitual criminal. The word "prison" as used in the Acts of 1887, Chapter 435, Section 1, is not limited to state prison but includes all places of imprisonment for crime. 

Wares, Petitioner, 161 Mass. 70

A child held in the public institution of the City of Boston under the Acts of 1881, Chapter 181, and Acts of 1886, Chapter 330, constitutional on decisions of the Institutions Commissioner, and a writ will not permit of a review. 

McConologue Case, 107 Mass. 154

A writ will issue for a minor who had enlisted in the United 

States Army. 

Fleming v. Clark, 12 Allen, 191

The court will not discharge on a writ a person committed in a sentence of the superior court on which it appears there is no error. 

Com. v. Briggs, 16 Pick. 203

A writ may be issued against a wife on the application of her husband for the purpose of obtaining the custody of the child. 

The husband and wife were living apart without any divorce or separate support proceedings. 

Randall v. Bridge, 2 Mass. 549 

Com. v. Brickett, 8 Pick. 138

It is the intention of the habeas act to relieve against unlawful imprisonment and not to alter the law authorizing commitments. 

In re Riley, 19 Mass. 172

The proper remedy for an illegal sentence is a writ of error. 

Jones v. Robbins, 74 Mass. 329

If a court of special or limited jurisdiction exceeds its authority and issues a warrant of commitment, the judgment is void and not voidable, and the commitment under it is illegal and may be inquired into by a writ of habeas corpus. If it be a court of general jurisdiction, this is not so. 

Com. v. Sumner, 5 Pick. 360

If a person be unlawfully arrested and held on bail on a writ, he may be relieved on a writ of habeas corpus.

CRIMINAL CASES 

In re Sennott, 146 Mass. 489

A prisoner confined on sentence will not be released on habeas for errors in the mittimus when the court has the judgment before it.

HACKNEY CARRIAGE 

Com. v. Flax, 259 Mass. 117

The state need not prove beyond a reasonable doubt that the officer was acting in good faith, or that it was necessary to move the vehicle to avoid congestion or to promote safety and convenience. The officer arrested the defendant without a warrant at the time of the commission of the offense, but the legality of the arrest was not argued or decided. 

Com. v. Page, 155 Mass. 227 

A hackney carriage kept in a stable comes within the law that no person shall use a hackney carriage without a license. 

Com. v. White, 260 Mass. 300

The complaint charged "did engage in the business of transporting persons for hire." The defendant, licensed in Boston, took a fare from Boston to Brookline, and then took a passenger from one point in Brookline to another point in Brookline. Not guilty, as one act is not engaging in business. 

Morley v. Police Com'r., 261 Mass. 269

The police commissioner has the right to license hackney carriages and to revoke them, and it is not an arbitrary power. 

The refusal to occupy a stand assigned as unprofitable for a special stand is grounds for revocation. 

Com. v. Matthews, 122 Mass. 60 

No person having charge of a hackney carriage shall stand with it to solicit passengers in a place other than the place assigned to it. The driver is considered with it, even if he leaves it and is in a passenger station soliciting passengers. 

Com. v. Gage, 114 Mass. 328

A regulation forbidding the overcharge of a passenger is constitutional. The passenger and the driver cannot make a special contract which is in excess of the legal fare.

CRIMINAL CASES 

257 

Com. v. Duane, 98 Mass. 1 

A driver of a hackney carriage is precluded from making a special contract with a passenger for a higher rate of fare for the service than the regulations permit. 

Com. v. Brooks, 99 Mass. 434

No person transgresses an ordinance who does not voluntarily suffer his vehicle to stop in the street more than the prescribed time. 

Com. v. Haydock, 286 Mass. 47

Under the Acts of 1931, Chapter 408, Section 7, as amended by Chapter 159A of the General Laws, a hackney carriage driver must have two licenses, one by the police commissioner and one by the common council, if he is to compete with a common carrier. Picking up passengers along a fixed route. This statute is not limited in its operation to motor vehicles carrying passengers for hire in interstate business between fixed and regular termini. 

HAWKER AND PEDDLER GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 101 ACTS OF 1935, CHAP. 42 

Com. v. Lagorio, 141 Mass. 81

A person who is licensed as a hawker and peddler is not authorized to violate a lawful police regulation of a city. 

Com. v. Hana, 195 Mass. 262

Goods in a suitcase and offered for sale by a hawker and peddler although not opened, are exposed for sale under the statute.

Com. v. Crowell, 156 Mass. 215 

A party may be engaged temporarily or transiently in one city or town, while having a permanent place of business in another town. So far as he is engaged in selling temporarily and transiently, he comes within the prohibition of the statute, without any regard to the fact that he is also carrying on an establishment and a permanent business elsewhere. Whether his sole business is selling temporarily and transiently, or whether he does it more or less frequently in connection with the permanent business at a fixed place or places, does not matter; he comes in either case within the statute.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Anderson, 272 Mass. 100

The act of selling or offering for sale religious books and pamphlets from door to door in a town is a violation of Chapter 101, Section 14. The mere fact that the defendant derived no gain or that the books were educational and religious is not any defense. 

Com. v. Farnum, 114 Mass. 267

An agent for sewing machines who goes from place to place exhibiting a sample machine and taking orders and delivering them himself on conditional contracts of sale sent to him by his company, although he occasionally fills an order by the delivery of the sample, is not a hawker and peddler. 

Com. v. Ober, 12 Cush. 493

It is not a violation for an agent to deliver to traders in the country goods made in Boston which had previously been ordered, nor to deliver a larger quantity than was ordered. 

Pelosi v. Bugbee, 217 Mass. 579

Selling on installments is within the law. Hawkers and peddlers have the right to peddle ice, and a city cannot pass an ordinance abridging their right. 

Com. v. Fenton, 139 Mass. 195

A hawker's and peddler's license does not entitle the holder to violate a city ordinance. 

Com. v. Newhall, 164 Mass. 338

A traveling troupe that gives a show and sells medicine, hires a hall and occupies the same for two weeks is an itinerant vendor. 

The law of Chapter 101 is not a revenue act and is within the police power of the state. 

Com. v. Cusick, 120 Mass. 183

If a hawker and peddler goes about exposing his wares and merchandise, he must have his name printed on them even if he does not own or use a vehicle. The penalty under Section 27 has reference to not exhibiting license, but may be punished un der the provisions of Section 14. 

Com. v. Bruckheimer, 14 Gray, 29

A petty chapman is a hawker and peddler. A complaint must allege that the defendant "as such hawker and peddler did peddle goods, etc."

CRIMINAL CASES 

259 

Com. v. Woods, 225 Mass. 78

Section 257 of Chapter 94 of the General Laws does not apply to a wagon sent out by an ice company to deliver ice on contracts previously made. 

Com. v. Fox, 218 Mass. 498

The legislature may delegate to a local board or officer the power to establish rules touching business in which the public interests may be affected; the words "and not elsewhere in the city" do not demand an unqualified utter prohibition of the business of a hawker and peddler. 

Com. v. Morrison, 197 Mass. 199

Under the Acts of 1908, Chapter 360, a lunch cart that occupied a street was a nuisance. Now changed by statute.

Indictment for hawking and peddling goods not of the product of the United States and the state must prove that the goods were of foreign make. Declared unconstitutional. Com. v.

Tea and coffee are not provisions under Section 17 of Chapter 101 of the General Laws.

HIGHWAY, DEFINITION OF A 

Com. v. Matthews, 122 Mass. 60

There was no legal evidence of the laying out of Providence Street by the city or of its formal acceptance, but a witness testified that he had known the street for twenty years to be a common thoroughfare for the public, and that the street had been paved and lighted by the city during that time, and that it had been called Providence Street during that time. To this evidence the defendant objected but the court admitted it and ruled "that if the jury found that this street had been used by the public as a street for twenty years and that the city had lighted and repaired it and during this time it had been called Providence Street, it was evidence from which the jury might infer it had been located and dedicated to the public use as a street and that it was Providence Street."

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Mara, 250 Mass. 209

A "way," as defined in Chapter 90 of the General Laws, is any public highway, private way laid out under the authority of the statute, way dedicated to public use, or way under the control of park commissioners or body having like powers. The evidence tended to prove that the street was well lighted, over 36 feet in width between the curbs, with a surface of concrete or cement, and with cross roads leading from it. There were houses on both sides and a considerable amount of traffic in both directions. The street was referred to as the main road to 

Lenox, and the defendant testified that he had driven up and down the same for eight years. Some of the witnesses spoke of it as a highway. The plan used as a chart showed the location of a hydrant and the tracks of a street railway in the center. 

Upon this evidence the street appeared to be an important city street and there was nothing in the evidence to suggest that it was not a way within the meaning of the statute quoted. Curbings, concrete paving, electric lights, and hydrants in a street are commonly the result of the expenditures of the public money for the public use and convenience. Street railway tracks apart from the public authority are located in public ways. (General 

Laws, Chapter 161, Section 54.) It would be highly improbable that the main road from Pittsfield to Lenox should be a private way. The jury had the right to infer from the testimony that the street was a way within the meaning of the statute. This case follows the case of Com. v. Leone, 250 Mass. 515, in which there was not any evidence to show that a street came to be established as a public way, and we see no reason for changing the view of the court. 

Jennings v. Inhab. of Tisbury, 5 Gray, 73

Evidence of general, uninterrupted public use of a road as a highway for twenty years is sufficient to charge a town with liability to keep it in repair, notwithstanding the statute which provides that no way by dedication shall be made chargeable upon a town. To establish such a way when there is no proof of dedication and where the element of dedication does not subsist, it will be necessary to prove actual public use, general uninterrupted continued use for a certain length of time. In general, it must be such as to warrant a presumption of laying out, dedication, or appropriation, by parties having authority so to lay out, or a right to appropriate like that of prescription or nonappearing grant in case of individuals. It stands upon the

CRIMINAL CASES 

261 same legal ground, a presumption that whatever was necessary to give the act legal effect and operation was rightly done though no other evidence of it can now be produced except the actual enjoyments of the benefits conferred by it. See Com. v. Coupe, 128 Mass. 68.

HOUSE OF ILL FAME GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 139, SECS. 4-13 GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 272, SEC. 24 

Com. v. Lavonsair, 132 Mass. 1

The difference between a nuisance under Chapter 139 and an iron clad so-called house of ill fame under Section 24 of Chapter 272. One consists of keeping any place, the other of keeping a particular place. Both are nuisances. One is punished as a common nuisance and must be alleged as such, the other is punished as a house of ill fame and need not be alleged as a nuisance. 

Com. v. Bagdasarian, 257 Mass. 248

Conversation with an inmate of a disorderly house in the absence of the defendant in regard to bargaining is admissible. 

Com. v. Harwood, 4 Gray, 41

Conversation held by men immediately after coming out of a house, not in the presence of the defendant or inmates, as to what had taken place inside of the house, is inadmissible. 

Com. v. Kimball, 7 Gray, 328

The character of the women in the house and the character of their conversation in the house were direct and pertinent evidence of the character of the house. 

Com. v. Ashley, 2 Gray, 356

The pleader need not aver that the house was resorted to by divers persons or that the defendant kept it for lucre. 

Com. v. Gallagher, 83 Mass. 592

Proof that a nuisance was kept and maintained for a space of two hours is sufficient to support a conviction. 

Com. v. Lambert, 12 Allen, 177

The permission by the keeper of the house of a single act of illicit intercourse within it does not of itself constitute the

CRIMINAL CASES 

keeping of a house of ill fame "resorted to" for prostitution and lewdness. 

Com. v. Cobb, 120 Mass. 356

The state must prove that during some substantial part of the time averred the defendant had in whole or in part the control and management of the premises. 

Com. v. Lambert, 12 Allen, 177 "Lewdness" means illicit intercourse, whether public or private, and not merely public indecency. 

Com. v. Gannett, 83 Mass. 7

It is sufficient to support a complaint for keeping a house of ill fame if the defendant has only aided and assisted others in keeping the same. 

Com. v. Moore, 11 Cush. 600 

Com. v. Harrington, 3 Pick. 26

In a complaint for letting a house for the purpose of ill fame the indictment must give the name of the lessee or state some reason for not so doing, and allege some date as the date of making the lease. 

Com. v. Clark, 145 Mass. 251 

Com. v. Kimball, 73 Mass. 328

Evidence of the general reputation of the inmates and frequenters is admissible.

HUSBAND AND WIFE 

Nonsupport of. 

Com. v. Booth, 266 Mass. 80

It makes no difference where the defendant lives, he is bound to support his wife where she is, where she had the right to leave him and seek her own domicile. 

Pease v. Pease, 237 Mass. 563 The filing of a libel in the superior court does not deprive the probate court of jurisdiction of a petition previously filed. 

If the superior court has not made any order, the probate court can enter a decree. 

Buckman v. Buckman, 176 Mass. 229

The husband and wife are living in the same house; she is performing some of the duties of a wife. The court has the power to make an order for separate support.

CRIMINAL CASES 

263 

Com. v. Herrick, 263 Mass. 25

The court must find that the defendant was physically able to work and could obtain employment. They were in the state for a period of four days previous to the date of the complaint, and even if he was domiciled in another state he becomes, on his voluntary return to this state, subject during that period to its laws. 

Com. v. Shannon, 223 Mass. 62

The dismissal of an annulment proceeding; although the husband and wife never had sexual intercourse with each other, the husband can be convicted of nonsupport.

Tasker v. Stahley, 153 Mass. 148 

For a married woman to leave her husband without cause is not a great crime. It is legal, if with his consent; and if against his will, it is only illegal in the sense that if she keeps away from him for three years he may get a divorce. A married woman must be supposed to be capable of receiving advice to separate from her husband without losing her reason of responsibility. 

The statute wisely does not attempt to require a man to live with his wife against his will, any more than the law requires a woman to live with her husband.

Ford v. Ford, 143 Mass. 577 

The wife deserted her husband without excuse more than three years before filing of a divorce by the husband for desertion and was never willing to return to him. Within three years from the desertion the husband had reason to believe her guilty of adultery and filed a divorce on that ground. He stated that he was always willing to take her back. Libel dismissed. A separation by consent cannot ripen into desertion or abandonment by mere failure to support without an attempt by the wife at reconciliation.

Peabody v. Peabody, 104 Mass. 195 

The mere neglect of a husband, with no circumstances of aggravation, to provide maintenance for his wife and children for fifteen years, during which she has supported the children from her earnings, is not such gross or wanton and cruel neglect as will sustain a libel in her behalf for a divorce. 

Mulhern v. McDavitt, 16 Gray, 404

A husband who receives into his family the children of his wife by a former marriage stands to them in loco parentis, and,

CRIMINAL CASES 

in the absence of an express contract or of circumstances showing a different arrangement, has a right to their services and is liable for their support and education. A husband was not by the common law obliged to support the children of his wife by a former marriage. 

Brookfield v. Inh. of Warren, 128 Mass. 287

A man is under no legal obligation to support his stepchild, and the fact that such child receives aid from the town as a pauper upon the application of the stepfather will not make the latter a pauper. 

Livingston v. Hammond, 162 Mass. 375

A man is not bound to maintain the children of his wife by a former marriage; but if he chooses to receive them into his family and to assume the parent to them in their daily life, the law will not imply a contract on his part to pay them for services which they render him while members of his family, nor a contract on their part to pay him for their maintenance. 

Coakley's Case, 216 Mass. 71 

"Parent" commonly means lawful mother or father by blood. 

It does not lend itself readily to a significance so broad as to include stepfather or stepmother or anyone standing in loco parentis. 

Dolan v. Brooks, 168 Mass. 350

The fact that the wife has an income of her own does not relieve the husband from the obligation to support her. 

Com. v. Graham, 157 Mass. 73

An infant son is entitled to his own wages so far as they are necessary for his own support and that of his wife and children, even if he married without the consent of his father. The marriage of an infant son with the consent of his father works an emancipation. It is the same as if he married without the consent. When an infant daughter marries she is emancipated from the control of her parents. Therefore a husband may be guilty of nonsupport even if he is a minor. 

Com. v. Shaman, 223 Mass. 62

A decree dismissing the libel of a husband for annulling the marriage establishes the status of husband and wife, and cannot be attacked collaterally by the husband in his attempted defense in a complaint against him for nonsupport. Only her

CRIMINAL CASES 

265 conduct since the marriage was admissible in justification of the defendant's failure to support her. 

Tonroos v. White, 220 Mass. 336

It has been the tendency of decisions and the positive trend of Massachusetts statutes to ameliorate the common law disabilities of married women. She now stands before the law almost, if not quite, on the same footing as the husband as to all property rights, domestic privileges, and family immunities, though not charged with equal responsibilities. In case of discord or separation she has the same rights as her husband to custody of children. In Chapter 208, Sections 29, 30, 31, and

Chapter 209, Sections 36 and 37, she is made responsible for proper support of her children equally with her husband, except that she is exempt from the liability of criminal prosecution in this regard which rests upon the husband. Chapter 273 imposes upon husbands and wives penalties for criminal neglect of their children, while the Uniform Desertion Act makes either the father or mother liable to severe punishment for desertion or willful refusal properly to care for and rear their children. 

Gleason v. City of Boston, 144 Mass. 25

Neither at common law or under the statutes is a married woman whose husband is living, under legal obligation to support their children, even if the husband is imprisoned for a crime. It was not intended that the father and the mother should be jointly responsible; she became so only when he has ceased to be. 

Somerset v. Dighton, 12 Mass. 383

The laws of the province previous to A.D. 1767 which required the warning of persons out of town to prevent their acquiring a settlement did not extend to minors although illegitimate; it could not be presumed to have been the intent of the legislature to remove infant children from the custody and protection of their parents. 

Silverman v. Silverman, 140 Mass. 560

Where a husband and wife enter into an agreement between themselves it is not enforcible and is void. 

Prescott v. Webster, 175 Mass. 316

The general rule is that when a wife lives apart from her husband without any fault on her part, the husband is liable

CRIMINAL CASES 

for her adequate maintenance in her station in life, and in case he fails in that duty he is liable to any third person for necessaries furnished to the wife, but the husband is not liable if the wife has property adequate for her support. This is at most a question of fact and not of law. 

McGeary v. McGeary, 181 Mass. 539

A father who is guardian of his minor child and who is not of sufficient pecuniary ability to support the child suitably should be allowed a reasonable charge for the support of the child in the settlement of his account as guardian. 

Hunter v. Boucher, 3 Pick. 289

Where a person turns away his wife for the cause of adultery he is not liable on her contracts made with persons having notice that he has discarded her. Whether the want of such notice, or a divorce refused on the grounds of like criminality on the part of the husband would make any difference, quaere. 

Hancock v. Merrick, 10 Cush. 41

A wife who leaves her husband for cause carries with her credit, and he is liable for necessaries furnished her by a third person. But he will not be liable in such case for the board of the child the custody of which is awarded to the mother and which lives with her. 

Mayher v. Thayer, 8 Gray, 172

A husband whose wife has left him with his express or implied consent or in consequence of his cruelty and neglect is bound to pay for necessaries furnished her while so absent until he goes or sends for her to return. 

Burlen v. Shannon, 14 Gray, 433

A right of action against the husband for the board of his wife after she has justifiably left him is not barred by the fact that one A and the wife conspired to abduct and conceal a minor child of the husband in order to compel him to settle a separate support and maintenance upon his wife. 

Benjamin v. Dockham, 132 Mass. 181

An action for the price of milk delivered to the wife against her husband's express request while she was living apart from him cannot be maintained against the husband in the absence of evidence that she was living apart from him under such cir-

CRIMINAL CASES 

267 cumstances as to give her implied authority to bind him by contract for necessities. 

Sturbridge v. Franklin, 160 Mass. 149

In an action by a town against a husband for support furnished his wife as a pauper while she is living apart from him, the burden is on the plaintiff to prove that she is living apart for justifiable reasons. The commencement of proceedings for divorce by a husband against his wife on the grounds of cruelty towards him will not of itself justify her in leaving the house and enable her to carry his credit with her if he is willing to provide support for her there. 

Newman's Case, 222 Mass. 563 

Where a husband and wife separate by mutual consent because at the time he was only earning $11 a week, was not supporting her as she needed to be supported, and where thereafter the husband and wife continued to live apart for a number of years until the husband died when he was earning $21.60 a week, the wife cannot be found to have been living apart from her husband for justifiable reasons at the time of his death under the workingman's compensation act. 

Watts v. Watts, 160 Mass. 464

A husband discovered his wife in the act of adultery and ejected her from his house. She subsequently brought a petition in the probate court for separate support. The husband appeared and defended against it but offered no evidence of his wife's adultery. The probate court entered a decree in favor of the wife which recited that she "for justifiable cause was living apart actually from her husband." The question of the wife's adultery not having been necessarily involved and determined in the issue tried in the probate court, the judgment of that court was not conclusive against a divorce libel afterwards brought by the husband for the adultery of his wife.

De Ferrari v. De Ferrari, 220 Mass. 38 

A decree of a probate court appointing a man and his wife joint guardians of their minor child is superseded, so far as it relates to the custody of the child, by a decree of the superior court made in a divorce suit brought by the wife more than a year later, awarding to the wife the care and custody of the minor child and ordering the husband to make certain payments for the support of his wife and child. The superior court

CRIMINAL CASES 

when the man and wife are before it as the parties to a libel for divorce becomes the court of domestic relations in matters affecting the welfare of the family and the care, custody, and support of minor children.

Franklin v. Franklin, 190 Mass. 349 

In connection with the question of desertion the right of the husband to determine the place of domicile should be kept in mind. The fundamental fact to be considered is that the husband, as head of the family responsible for its support, has the right to choose and establish a domicile for himself and his wife and children. A refusal of the wife to stay with him at that domicile without a sufficient reason is desertion. The wife was in England with her husband; he came to America to better himself; she refused to leave; twice he sent her money for her to come and still she refused. She is guilty of desertion. 

Reynolds v. Reynolds, 3 Allen, 605

As to the right of a husband to refuse to support his wife after discovering that at the time of the marriage she was pregnant by another. The court has the power to declare a marriage void into which a man was induced to enter by confiding in the representations of the woman whom he took for his wife that she was chaste, when in fact she was with child by another man, if her husband repudiated her as soon as he had reason to know of the fact. 

Alley v. Winn, 134 Mass. 77

If a husband and wife live apart by mutual consent, the wife receiving a sum inadequate for her support and agreeing that she will release her dower in his land, thereafter support herself and make no claim upon him, he is not liable for necessaries furnished her, she not having after such arrangement between them made any claim on him for support or offered to return to him. 

Bailey v. Dillon, 186 Mass. 244 

Terkelson v. Peterson, 216 Mass. 531

A fair and voluntary contract between a wife and a trustee in behalf of her husband under which the husband places in the hands of the trustee a fund for the support of his wife and the wife agrees that she will not make any further claim on her husband for support is not void against public policy. What the effect of a bona fide offer on her part to return, quaere.

Glass v. Glass, 260 Mass. 562 

The domicile of a legitimate minor is that of the father, even if the parents are living apart.

The defendant is within this state and is an alien. The child is in Canada. The defendant is not living with his wife. The domicile of the defendant governs.

Form for neglect to support family. 

Stone v. Duffy, 219 Mass. 178

Husband and wife divorced. Both can have the custody of the child at stated periods. The father dies and the court appoints his sister the guardian of the child. The right of the mother in the child is only that of a surviving parent unaffected by the decree in the divorce suit.

Oliver v. Oliver, 151 Mass. 349 

A petition brought by the husband two years after divorce on a decree for desertion, for a change in the decree which gave the wife the custody of the minor children so as to afford him access to the child. There was important evidence from the appearance of the parents, also the manner, as well as other witnesses which could be represented in print. The judge ordered the decree modified so as to give the father the opportunity of seeing the child from time to time. The mother of children, especially young children, is almost invariably the proper custodian of them. 

Haskell v. Haskell, 152 Mass. 16

A husband obtaining a divorce from his wife for her bigamy and adultery is not entitled as a matter of law to the custody of the children. In this case the custody of the children was given to the wife although the divorce libel was against her. She could support them, were to remain in her custody, not to be

CRIMINAL CASES 

removed from the court's jurisdiction, and the father could see them with reasonable frequency. 

Reynolds v. Sweetser, 15 Gray, 78 

Hancock v. Merrick, 10 Cush. 41

Even where a wife is living apart from her husband for justifiable cause, i.e., consent or misconduct, it does not follow that the husband may not show that the children would be tolerably safe with him. If a wife leaves her husband's house by reason of cruelty, and from reasonable apprehension for her safety, he is liable for her board; and also for the board of the child whom she takes with her, if, knowing where the child is, he makes no attempt to reclaim it, and he is not discharged from such liability by his wife's subsequent return to the home. 

The duty of support as dependent upon the right of custody. 

Baldwin v. Foster, 138 Mass. 449

If a wife leaves her husband without justifiable cause, taking their child with her, and the husband is willing and able to support the child, and so informs the wife and a third person with whom she places the child, the fact that the father makes no attempt to obtain the custody of the child does not of itself authorize the wife to pledge his credit for necessaries furnished to the child by such a third person at the request of the wife. 

Foss v. Hartwell, 168 Mass. 66 

If a child, having been taken away from its father without right, by the mother who has married another man, voluntarily elects, when the choice is given him, to stay with his mother in her new home, and her husband chooses to receive him without communicating with his father, he has no right to look to the father for support. 

Brow v. Brightman, 136 Mass. 187

A father is not liable for the support of his minor child after the custody has been given to the mother by the decree of the court. He had no right to take the child, and support it himself or employ anyone else to support it without the consent of the mother. The wife had no authority to bind the father by contract to support the child. The mother could petition the court to modify the decree. 

Malden Hospital v. Murdock, 218 Mass. 73

No action can be maintained by a third person against the husband for the value of necessaries furnished the wife who is

CRIMINAL CASES 

271 living apart from him by mutual consent, if, before the necessaries were furnished, the probate court on the petition of the wife had ordered the husband to pay a certain sum to the wife periodically for her support, and that order remained in force and was complied with by the husband. 

Purington v. Jamrock, 195 Mass. 187

The right of property which parents have in their minor children is subject to their correlative duty to care for and protect their children, and the law secures that right only so long as they discharge their obligations. Where a child in the custody of the State Board of Charity is adopted legally by a person who is found to be suitable to have the custody of him and charge of his education, the child still remains in the custody of the board until he attains the age of twenty-one years, or that board shall discharge him from custody, when the object of his commitment shall have been accomplished. The custody of the child was taken from the mother for cause, she acquiesced in this, and suffered the state to support the child as a pauper. Under these circumstances her consent to the adoption was not necessary. 

Farnham v. Pierce, 141 Mass. 203 

Kelley, Petitioner, 152 Mass. 432

The commitment of a child to the overseers of the poor by a district court is constitutional. The court found that the child was growing up without salutary control, exposing it to lead an idle and dissolute life. The commitment is not conclusive upon the parent as an adjudication on his right to the custody of the child; the parent may show that the cause of the commitment has been removed and that he is competent to have again the custody of the child. 

Wares, Petitioner, 161 Mass. 70 

Where the Commissioners of Public Institutions, after a full and fair hearing, denied the petition of the mother of a minor child committed to their custody for its restoration to her, their action, where no error of law or neglect or unfaithfulness in the discharge of their duty is alleged, will not be reviewed on a writ of habeas corpus. This differs from the two above cases (Farnham v. Pierce, 141 Mass. 203, and Kelley, Petitioner, 152 Mass. 432) in that it was whether the rights of the father, who had no opportunity to be heard, were concluded by the findings of the district court, so that he could not be allowed to show that the subject of the commitment had been accomplished. In Kelley, Pe-

CRIMINAL CASES 

titioner, ubi supra it was whether the mere fact which was set up in answer to the Board of Charity, that in its object of commitment had not been accomplished, deprived the father without his having been heard upon the matter, of the right to show that the object of commitment had been accomplished, and that the child should be discharged.

Com. v. Dee, 222 Mass. 184 

A child of a widow, who is dependent upon public charity only by reason of the poverty of its mother without any culpability on her part, is not a neglected child within the meaning of the statute.

Jurisdiction. 

Pease v. Pease, 237 Mass. 563 

The filing of a libel in the superior court does not deprive the probate court of jurisdiction of a petition previously filed, and if the superior court has not made any order, the probate court can enter a decree. 

Com. v. Simmons, 165 Mass. 356

The defendant was complained of in the lower court for nonsupport. He afterwards filed a libel in the superior court which was dismissed. He claimed the superior court ousted the lower court. Not so.

Franklin v. Franklin, 190 Mass. 349 

A wife cannot legally refuse to accompany her husband in a reasonable change of domicile. 

Com. v. Herrick, 263 Mass. 25

A husband, even if he domiciled in another state, is subject to a complaint and conviction for nonsupport where it appears that for a period of four days preceding the date of the complaint both he and his wife were within the state. The complaint charged the offense as of a single day, Sept. 14, 1927. The defendant admitted he was actually present in Worcester for a day and a night in September 1924 and that the defendant was actually in this state from September 9 to September 16, 1927. Even if domiciled in another state he became on his voluntary return to the state subject during that period to its laws. 

Com. v. Booth, 266 Mass. 80

The place of residence of the husband is not of consequence in view of the fact that the husband left his wife here and went

CRIMINAL CASES 

273 into another state, and she rightfully remained and is now in this state. The place of need of the wife fixes jurisdiction of the crime, even though the husband may not be there at the moment her need arises. Laws to enforce the natural obligation of a husband to support his wife and family would be in vain if the husband by crossing a state line could avoid the possibility of enforcement. The people were married here and the husband was convicted here of adultery. She continued to live here, he went to 

Ohio, where he failed to get a divorce, the court order in Ohio was to make payments to his wife which the husband did. He afterwards moved to Indiana, where he obtained a divorce, the wife never receiving any notice. The courts in view of the method of obtaining the divorce did not recognize the divorce and he was convicted of nonsupport in Massachusetts. 

Com. v. Ackers, 197 Mass. 91

The defendant is here in this state. The child is in Canada. 

The defendant is not living with his wife and the defendant is an alien. The domicile of the defendant governs. 

Com. v. Shannon, 223 Mass. 62

The dismissal of an annulment proceeding, though the husband and the wife never had sexual intercourse with each other, does not prevent the husband from being convicted of nonsupport of his wife. 

Com. v. Maloney, 145 Mass. 205

A defendant pleaded guilty and the magistrate continued the case to a day in the future on payment of costs, to be called up for sentence on notice to the defendant. This was done, the defendant was sentenced, and no appeal was taken. The magistrate had no authority to continue the case indefinitely, so a commitment or a recognizance would be void. 

Finer v. Com., 250 Mass. 493

The defendant was sentenced under a suspended sentence and placed on probation with no definite time set for the probation period. The defendant was surrendered and sentenced on the original sentence. Although the statute says that a definite term is required, the fact that there was not any does not affect the sentence and the suspended sentence, which was legal. When a case is pending in a court of general jurisdiction in which continuances are from term to term, the defendant may waive the formal entries for continuance and consent that the case may

CRIMINAL CASES 

remain in court without such entries until asked for by either party. Coercion. 

Com. v. Burk, 11 Gray, 437

If the husband is near enough for the wife to act under his immediate influence and control, though not in the same room, he was not absent within the meaning of the law. 

Whipple v. Whipple, 181 Mass. 343 

Presumption of coercion in a complaint for maintaining a house of ill fame does not arise. 

Com. v. Adams, 186 Mass. 101

The presumption that a crime committed by a wife in the presence of her husband as the crime of the husband is not conclusive. Evidence of her conduct in his presence may be sufficient to rebut. Crimes forbidden by the laws of nature which are mala in se and where the wife may be the principal agent. 

Com. v. Hopkins, 133 Mass. 381

A married woman may be convicted of keeping a disorderly house if she acts of her own free will and without the coercion of her husband. 

Com. v. Roberts, 132 Mass. 267

A married woman was complained of for keeping a nuisance; she may be convicted of the proof of illegal sales in the absence of her husband in a dwelling house occupied by them and hired by him. Some of the sales were made while the husband was in the house. 

Com. v. Hill, 145 Mass. 305

The husband was complained of for keeping a liquor nuisance. 

The title of the house was in the name of the wife; the defendant lived with her; she furnished the food; the defendant had no business but had money of his own. The indictment was from January 1 to June 10. The defendant could introduce evidence that for five years previous to January 1 he had tried to influence his wife to desist. 

Com. v. Munsey, 112 Mass. 287

That a wife in the absence of her husband does a criminal act even in obedience to his orders her coverture is not a defense. 

It must appear that he was present at the time; if he was near enough so that she was under his control, this is sufficient.

275 

Com. v. Feeney, 13 Allen, 560

If a wife does a criminal act in her husband's absence though by his orders, her coverture is no defense. 

Com. v. Butler, 1 Allen, 4

There is no legal presumption that acts done by the wife in her husband's absence are done under his direction and control; indeed, if she in his absence does a criminal act even by his order or procurement, she is guilty. 

Com. v. Wood, 97 Mass. 225

A woman owned the house and furniture, the husband exercised acts of control and management, she also lived there and carried on the business. Guilty. 

Com. v. Welch, 97 Mass. 593

The conviction of the husband of a nuisance is not a bar to the conviction of wife, although the testimony is the same in both cases. The fact that she furnished the liqour which she sold is no defense for coercion. 

Conversations between. 

Jacobs v. Hessler, 113 Mass. 157

Conversations between husband and wife in the presence of their children, who are shown not to have taken part or listened to it, is a private conversation. 

Com. v. Griffin, 110 Mass. 181

Two police officers concealed themselves in a cell adjoining the cells of a husband and wife and listened to their conversation. A private conversation between husband and wife who thought no one could overhear them can be testified to by a concealed listener. 

Freeman v. Freeman, 238 Mass. 150

Conversations in a street where it appears that no passersby paid any attention is admissible. In the presence of a nine-yearold child is a question for the court. 

Com. v. Cronin, 185 Mass. 96 

Com. v. Jardine, 143 Mass. 567

A wife in testifying against her husband cannot relate a private conversation. 

French v. French, 14 Gray, 186 

Abuse words addressed to a wife are admissible.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Barker, 185 Mass. 324 

Com. v. Saltzman, 258 Mass. 109

Either can testify against the other in a criminal trial if the one testifying is willing. 

Com. v. Baronian, 235 Mass. 364

Conversations that occurred before the marriage can be testified to after the marriage. 

Com. v. Moore, 162 Mass. 441

The defendant was in the dock. The wife testified in court in the presence of her husband. The defendant can be convicted; the wife is not acting under his coercion. 

Linnell v. Linnell, 249 Mass. 51

A daughter who is in the adjoining room twelve feet distant with the door open makes the conversation admissible. The husband and wife in a waiting room, people going and coming, makes it admissible. 

Fay v. Guynon, 131 Mass. 31

Conversation between a husband and wife in the presence of a third person who hears only a part of it is not a private conversation. 

Com. v. Caponi, 155 Mass. 534

Letters between husband and wife are admissible against the husband. The statute only excludes conversation. 

Com. v. Rosenblatt, 219 Mass. 197

A married woman may testify as to the legitimacy of her child. Practice has passed under the Acts of 1913; the law covers evidence that a mother may testify in a proceeding in which a husband is not a party. 

Com. v. Cleary, 152 Mass. 491

A husband charged with a crime cannot prove in defense the effect of a private conversation between himself and his wife. 

At the trial for exposing and keeping liquor for sale which the defendant contended was the property of his wife, evidence of the acts of the defendant after the liquor had been seized and after the trial in the lower court showing he was opposed to her selling and owning it is admissible.

Divorce. 

Maloof v. Abdullah, 218 Mass. 21

Sewall v. Sewall, 122 Mass. 156 

Langwald v. Langwald, 234 Mass. 269

A party who goes into another state and obtains a divorce for a cause which occurred here in Massachusetts, is still married under the laws of Massachusetts. 

Murphy v. Murphy, 249 Mass. 552 

Wright v. Wright, 264 Mass. 453 

Palmer v. Palmer, 265 Mass. 242

Parties were divorced in Massachusetts. One of the parties before the libel became absolute went into Rhode Island and there contracted a marriage to overcome the Massachusetts obstacle. The marriage is legal here, notwithstanding Section 10 

of Chapter 207 of the General Laws. 

Com. v. Graham, 157 Mass. 73

A minor goes into another state and there marries without the consent of his father with the intention to evade the Massachusetts laws. He returns. He is complained of here for nonsupport. An infant husband is entitled to his own wages even if he marries without the consent of his parents. When a minor contracts a marriage he becomes emancipated. 

Zeitlan v. Zeitlan, 202 Mass. 205 

Greene v. Greene, 2 Gray, 361

The superior court has no power to vacate a decree for divorce after it has become absolute, where the court had jurisdiction to grant the decree, even though the decree was made because of perjured testimony knowingly procured by the libelant, and because of gross fraud practiced upon the court. 

Kopigian v. Minassian, 212 Mass. 412

The dissolution of marriage in another country will be recognized by the court of this state. 

Thompson v. Thompson, 114 Mass. 566 

A man from whom a divorce had been obtained marries again in this state without leave of the court. The cohabitation is illegal from the beginning and a solemnization of it does not render it legal. It could only become so after having obtained leave to marry again.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Divorce elsewhere can enforce orders here. 

Dorey v. Dorey, 248 Mass. 359

A divorce procured elsewhere in another state to which the wife did not consent to the jurisdiction, she being domiciled in this state, does not bar the husband from being compelled to carry out the orders of the probate court in this state. 

Property rights. 

Com. v. Williams, 7 Gray, 337

Personal property in the possession of a married woman is to be presumed, in the absence of other evidence, to be the property of the husband, and must be described as the property of the husband in an indictment for the larceny of it. Now under the statute it can be alleged as her property if she has the cus tody or possession of it. Chapter 273, Section 9, of the General 

Laws. 

Nolin v. Peterson, 191 Mass. 283

Liabilities and rights of married women discussed. 

Bacon v. B. R. Ry., 256 Mass. 30

As a matter of law the legal surname of a woman after her marriage is that of her husband. 

Hawkins v. Prov. Ry. Co., 119 Mass. 596

Personal apparel furnished the wife by her husband or purchased by the wife with the consent of the husband with money given her from a common fund formed from their joint earnings remains the property of the husband, and the wife cannot maintain an action for the loss thereof. 

Duggan v. Wright, 157 Mass. 228

The money of a married woman does not become the property of her husband merely by the possession of it as her agent. 

Com. v. Peretz, 212 Mass. 253

A husband can be convicted of deriving support from the earnings of a prostitute even if that prostitute is his wife. 

Burt v. Burt, 168 Mass. 204

Drunkenness caused by the excessive use of drugs is not a cause for divorce; same discussed.
