279 

Com. v. Shannon, 223 Mass. 62

The dismissing of a marriage by annulment and although the husband and wife never had sexual intercourse with each other, the wife can maintain an action for nonsupport. 

Com. v. Manley, 12 Pick. 173

Property willed to a wife or given her becomes the property of the husband. 

Presumption of death. See also under Polygamy. 

Com. v. Hayden, 163 Mass. 453

An honest and reasonable belief that a former husband or wife is dead is not a defense to an indictment for polygamy. 

Whippen v. Whippen, 147 Mass. 294

A husband whose wife deserted him for more than seven years goes through a form of marriage and cohabits with another woman; is guilty of adultery. He is precluded from obtaining a divorce for such desertion, there being no presumption of his wife's death.

Impotency - Access to. 

Taylor v. Whittier, 240 Mass. 514

A defendant (husband) cannot bastardize his own child by testifying as to his impotency. A husband and wife cannot testify as to nonaccess between them. 

Hemmenway v. Towner, 1 Allen, 209 

Com. v. Bird, 264 Mass. 485

Every child born in wedlock, when the husband is not shown to be impotent, is presumed to be legitimate, even though the parties are living apart by mutual consent; that this presumption may be rebutted by proof that the husband had no access to his wife during the time according to nature he could be the father, but that presumption cannot be rebutted by proof of the wife's adultery while cohabitating with her husband, the law not allowing admission of evidence on the question whether the adulterer or the husband is most likely to be the father of the child. 

Joint defendants. 

Com. v. Cheney, 114 Mass. 281

At common law and also under the statute a married woman may be indicted and on appropriate evidence convicted sepa-

CRIMINAL CASES 

rately or jointly with her husband of keeping a house of ill fame even if he resided with her in the house. 

Com. v. Tryon, 99 Mass. 442

The husband and wife may be convicted jointly of a nuisance. 

She may be convicted of participation in the absence of her husband and not appearing to be done under the coercion of her husband. 

Com. v. Briggs, 5 Pick. 429

Husband and wife jointly indicted for receiving stolen goods. 

The declarations of the wife, although incriminating both, are admissible in evidence to prove her guilt, and the jury must take care that they do not operate to the prejudice of the husband. 

Com. v. Easland, 1 Mass. 15

The wife of one indicted and on trial jointly with others is not a competent witness for any of the defendants. They should have motioned for separate trials. 

When marriage occurs. 

Com. v. Munson, 127 Mass. 459

A ceremony performed by a man and a woman in good faith, no third person participating, no magistrate or any clerical person being present or officiating, neither a Friend or a Quaker, is not a valid marriage. 

Shutesbury v. Hadley, 133 Mass. 242

An attested copy of the town clerk's record of marriage, Chapter 207, Sections 41-49. 

Kennedy v. Doyle, 10 Allen, 161 

Com. v. Morris, 1 Cush. 391

A parish record of marriage, where the person making it is dead, but probably not a certificate of marriage. 

Com. v. Dill, 156 Mass. 226

A witness may testify that he was married to the female defendant and to the place where they were married without the production of the record of the marriage. 

Com. v. Hayden, 163 Mass. 453

A marriage may be proved by the testimony of either of the contracting parties. An attested copy of the record of the marriage from the records of the City Registrar certified to be an assistant Registrar is admissible in evidence.

CRIMINAL CASES 

281 

Com. v. Thompson, 99 Mass. 444

On the trial of a joint indictment for adultery of a man and woman which charges that at the time of the offense she was the wife of another man, her admission, in her paramour's presence that she was so is not competent evidence against him. 

Newburyport v. Boothbay, 9 Mass. 414 

Means v. Welles, 12 Met. 356

From a long-continued cohabitation of a man and woman as husband and wife a marriage may be presumed by the jury. 

Knower v. Wesson, 13 Met. 143

The statute which provides that where the fact of a marriage is required or offered to be proved, evidence of general repute, cohabitation as married persons, or other circumstantial or presumptive evidence from which the fact may be inferred, shall be competent evidence for consideration. Does not require that the evidence should come from the members or connections of the family of the parties whose marriage is in question; it may come from anyone who knows the circumstances. 

Randlett v. Rice, 141 Mass. 385

Cohabitation is evidence of marriage, but cohabitation is not marriage. 

Com. v. Hurley, 14 Gray, 411

Evidence that a woman occupied the same bed with the defendant in a tenement and was seen getting meals and doing other household duties there in his absence is competent evidence to prove her to be his wife. 

Raynham v. Canton, 3 Pick 293

A marriage de facto being proved. It should be presumed to be according to law as if a marriage was proven to have taken place in France, for instance, it should seem fit to require the party who denies the marriage to prove its validity. 

Com. v. Kenney, 120 Mass. 387

It appeared that the defendant, a Protestant, had been married in Ireland to a Roman Catholic by a priest, that he had cohabitated there with her as his wife, that he had afterwards, while his wife was living, been married to another in this state. 

The law of Ireland must be proved as a matter of fact, and that in the absence of evidence what that law was, the marriage there must be considered as legal. A marriage solemnized by a priest

CRIMINAL CASES 

and under which the parties have cohabitated as husband and wife is prima facie a valid marriage everywhere. 

Com. v. Rosenblatt, 219 Mass. 197

A mother, though married, is permitted to testify in proceedings in which her husband is not a party that some other man is the father of her child. 

Hemmenway v. Towner, 1 Allen, 209 

See under Impotency, access to. 

Phillips v. Alles, 2 Allen, 453

The presumption of the illegitimacy of a child of a married woman can only be rebutted by evidence which proves beyond all reasonable doubt that her husband cannot have been the father. A child born eight months after marriage will be presumed to be legitimate, although when born it has all the physical appearance of a full-grown and natural child, and proof of a statement by the mother that she had no connection with her husband before marriage and that her reputation for chastity was bad at the time of her marriage, and that for some months previously thereto she had been intimate with other men, if com- petent, is insufficient to rebut this presumption. 

Sullivan v. Kelley, 3 Allen, 148

In a complaint under the bastardy act seeking to charge one with being the father of a child begotten of a married woman while she was living with her husband, the presumption of legitimacy can only be rebutted by evidence which proves beyond all reasonable doubt that her husband could not have been the father. 

Koffman v. Koffman, 193 Mass. 593

The declaration of either parent is inadmissible to show that their children born after marriage are illegitimate. If, on a hearing on the objections of the wife to making absolute a decree nisi for a divorce obtained against her for cruel and abusive treatment, the wife shows that a child was born to her after the divorce was granted and that the date of its birth is consistent with its having been begotten by an act of intercourse which the wife set up before the decree nisi as condonation on the part of the husband, and the judge finds on the review of the evidence including the testimony of the husband, that there was no condonation abuse, there was not any act of intercourse between the husband and wife, at the time alleged and testified to by

CRIMINAL CASES 

283 the wife, this finding, although it may imply to adultery on the part of the wife, does not affect the legitimacy of the child as the evidence properly considered by the judge upon the issue of the condonation would not be competent to show that a child thus born in wedlock was legitimate. 

Com. v. Ellis, 160 Mass. 165

The record of conviction and sentence of a father on a complaint for unreasonably neglecting to provide support for his minor child is conclusive evidence that the paternity of the child was determined, and the father is estopped to set up the legitimacy of the child as a defense to a subsequent complaint for a similar offense.

IDLE AND DISORDERLY GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 272, SEC. 53 

Com. v. Tay, 170 Mass. 192

A married woman, even if supported by her husband or some other person, may be found guilty. 

Com. v. Diamond, 248 Mass. 511

A woman living in an apartment and having sexual intercourse with men who visited her there. A bill of particulars showed that she was a lewd, wanton and lascivious person in speech and behavior. A complaint charging her with being an idle and disorderly person is a good one. Under this decision rogues, vagabonds, beggars, lewd persons, and idle and disorderly persons come within the scope of this pleading. 

Com. v. Tomaselli, 257 Mass. 479

Evidence that the defendant did not work during the time alleged in the complaint. On one occasion solicited a woman to help him blackmail a man. One single act will not make the defendant an idle and disorderly person, but if the act was such that you might draw from that single act the inference that the man was doing that kind of work, was that kind of person, from that, then, you might and have the right to draw the inference that he was an idle and disorderly person. 

Com. v. Brown, 141 Mass. 78

The pleader need not allege that the defendant was under the necessity of laboring or supporting himself or following any business vocation. The statutory description is sufficient.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Doherty, 137 Mass. 245

The defendant was seen night and day in dance halls and drinking places and soliciting persons on the steps of a house of ill fame, was not engaged during that time in any lawful occupation or work. The jury could find that the defendant from her behavior did not possess independent means of support, that she was able to work but neglected so to do. 

Com. v. Hart, 137 Mass. 247

The state need not prove that the defendant had the opportunity to work, an offer which she refused. It is enough in connection with other evidence that she did not seek work and neglected all lawful business. 

Com. v. Carter, 108 Mass. 17

The defendant was an inmate of a house of ill fame, was seen walking on the streets, not doing anything except talking with other inmates of the house. She did not have any means of employment as far as the state could show, no means of support; all this occurring within a period of forty days. Enough to convict. 

Com. v. Norton, 13 Allen, 550

This crime can be joined with a complaint for being a common night walker. Idle and disorderly persons who misspend their time, etc., are different from idle persons who live without lawful employment and have no visible means of support. 

Com. v. Sullivan, 5 Allen, 511

If a defendant being under the necessity to work for the support of himself or persons dependent upon him, being able and having the opportunity to work neglects all lawful business, he is idle and disorderly if he frequents houses of ill fame, gambling houses, and tippling shops. If the jury found the defendant habitually misspends his time, he is guilty. The words "and is" do not change the character of the averment in à complaint. They may be rejected as surplusage. 

Com. v. Murray, 14 Gray, 397

Evidence of profanity is admissible in a complaint for being an idle and disorderly person; it is evidence of disorderly conduct and contrary to the good order of society.

285 

Jurisdiction. 

ILLEGITIMATE CHILDREN GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 273, SECS. 11-19 

Com. v. Dornes, 239 Mass. 592

The courts of this state have jurisdiction of an illegitimate child complaint for nonsupport of the child, although the mother's residence is in another state where the child was begotten and born. 

Com. v. Baxter, 267 Mass. 591 

Com. v. Ackers, 197 Mass. 91

The time and the place of the begetting are not material elements of the crime charged. A complaint received and sworn to in the West Roxbury district describes the complainant as of the City of Boston, and on the trial it developed that she lived within the district of the court. It is not defective merely because it does not contain the allegation that the defendant or the complainant lived within the district. 

McFadden v. Frye, 13 Allen, 472

If an illegitimate child is born in this state and both parents reside here at the time of birth, the father may be proceeded against no matter where the child was begotten. 

Willkams v. Campbell, 3 Met. 209

Where both parties to a complaint for the maintenance of a bastard child live within the state, such complaint may be brought and prosecuted in the county where one of the parties lives, although the child was begotten and born in another county. The birth of the child is certainly not the offense charged, and it is immaterial in what town or county the child was begotten or born. The prosecution may be commenced before the birth of the child, and it cannot be supposed that the legislature intended to leave the question as to the county in which the prosecution is to be commenced to depend on a future contingent event. 

Gallery v. Holland, 15 Gray, 50

A complaint under the bastardy act may be made and tried in the county in which the child is begotten and the respondent lives, although the complainant resides and the child is born in another county.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Garlick v. Bartlett, 4 Allen, 365

A complaint relating to the maintenance of bastard children may properly be made returnable before the police court of the town in which the child was born and the complainant lives, although the child was begotten in another town in the same county in which a police court is established. 

Kennedy v. McLellan, 193 Mass. 528

The complainant was a resident of Dorchester in the County of Suffolk, the defendant was a resident of the Town of Milton in the County of Norfolk. It is the superior court that has jurisdiction in these cases; the proceedings in the police, district and municipal courts are merely initiatory and it is quite sufficient if such a suit is brought to a hearing in the county or judicial district in which either the complainant or the defendant lives. 

Com. v. Kenney, 229 Mass. 157 

Proceedings in a bastardy case were dismissed on July 18, 1913. On February 23, 1918, the overseers of the poor petitioned to intervene to prosecute the complaint. Petition dismissed. 

Com. v. Mekelburg, 235 Mass. 383

The superior court has jurisdiction under the Acts of 1913, Chapter 563, Section 1 to prosecute by indictment against one who begets a woman with child, not being her husband. 

Day v. Day, 236 Mass. 362

One who is arrested for bastardy and pleads nolo contendere and the complaint is dismissed on the defendant marrying the girl cannot have the marriage annulled subsequently on the grounds of duress. 

Com. v. Sego, 125 Mass. 210

Good criminal pleading demands that the begetting be alleged to have taken place on and not on or about a certain day, and the allegation will be supported by proof of the begetting on any day. Adjudication. 

Com. v. Clark, 2 Mass. 155

The court must find that the defendant was the father of the bastard child before it can make an order against him for nonsupport.

CRIMINAL CASES 

287 

Com. v. Ellis, 160 Mass. 165

The adjudication of paternity serves several purposes. If it were not for this provision the only technical judgment from which an appeal might be taken in the lower court, and which, if not vacated by appeal would constitute a final adjudication of the question of paternity would be the sentence. A record of conviction and the sentence of a father for neglecting to provide support is conclusive evidence of the paternity of the child, and father is estopped from setting up illegitimacy to a second complaint. 

Young v. Makepeace, 103 Mass. 50

Whether a child was a full-time child may be testified to by any physician of ordinary experience who attended at the birth. 

The fact that the defendant was the father of the child may be established by a fair preponderance of evidence. Testimony as to the dissimilarity in the personal appearance between the child and one John Smith is inadmissible to rebut the evidence introduced by the defendant to show that John Smith and not himself was the father. 

Connolly v. Anderson, 112 Mass. 60

The final order of adjudication may be made upon default in the absence of the putative father. A mittimus issued by the court in a bastardy process to commit the absent defendant to prison until he gives a bond to perform the order will justify his arrest as well as his commitment. 

Corcoran v. Higgins, 194 Mass. 291 

Proceedings under the Revised Laws, Chapter 82, now Chapter 273 of the General Laws are in the nature of a civil proceeding. Pleading. 

Com. v. Sego, 125 Mass. 210

The pleader may allege the begetting to have taken place on a certain day and not on or about, and that allegation will be supported by proof of the begetting on any day. 

Bassett v. Abbott, 4 Gray, 69

A complaint which specifies the time and the place at which the child was begotten is supported by proof that the parties had intercourse at the time and place named, also at another time and place, and that the child was begotten by one of these acts.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Francis v. Rosa, 151 Mass. 532

On a complaint for illegitimate child the state need not prove that the child was begotten on the date alleged in the complaint. 

Statute of limitations. 

Wheelwright v. Greer, 10 Allen at 391

No statute of limitations bars a mother's right to complain against the father of her bastard child.

Ex post facto. 

Com. v. Callahan, 223 Mass. 250 

Com. v. Kinney, 229 Mass. 157

The 1913 Act (Illegitimate Child Act) is not ex post facto. 

Still born. 

Schramon v. Stephen, 133 Mass. 559

Under the old bastardy act a father was not liable in any manner for a still-born child but now under Chapter 273, Section 13, of the General Laws he is liable. 

Exhibiting the child. 

Scott v. Donovan, 154 Mass. 378 

Young v. Makepeace, 103 Mass. 30

The child in question may be exhibited to the court, and the court may continue the case on the charge of begetting until the child is born in order to learn the date of birth as compared to the date of the alleged intercourse. 

Workingman's compensation act. 

Olsen's Case, 247 Mass. 570 

An illegitimate child may be a dependent of the father, if living with him and dependent in whole or in part on his earnings. 

Civil and criminal both. 

Hill v. Wells, 6 Pick. 104 

Com. v. Mekelburg, 235 Mass. 383 

Prince v. Gudenay, 157 Mass. 417

It partakes of the nature of both civil and criminal, and the rules of evidence can be introduced. Evidence and similarity discussed.

289 

Evidence. 

Sullivan v. Hurley, 147 Mass. 387

Letters written by the defendant to the complainant after the begetting and before the delivery which show suspicious intimacy are admissible. 

Com. v. Brophy, 235 Mass. 438 

A complaint alleged May 1919 as the date of the begetting of an illegitimate child. Letters written in 1918 are admissible to establish and characterize the intimacy of the parties. 

Beers v. Jackman, 103 Mass. 192

Letters from the defendant to the woman showing an intimacy between them are admissible. Antecedent conduct as well as subsequent conduct and declarations can be introduced if they have a tendency to prove this fact. 

Noonan v. Bogan, 3 Allen, 481

A jury may find to be guilty of being the father upon the evidence of the mother alone, in connection with the record of the preliminary proceedings in the case. 

Com. v. Callahan, 223 Mass. 150

The father can be convicted even if no demand is made, and if the woman married and has a husband supporting her. 

Com. v. Hunt, 243 Mass. 286 

The complainant cannot be asked whether she had other children after she had previously testified that she never was married. 

Sullivan v. Kelley, 3 Allen, 148 

Charging one with being the father of an illegitimate child with a married woman while she was living with her husband, the presumption of legitimacy can only be rebutted by evidence which proves beyond all reasonable doubt that her husband could not have been the father. 

Baxter v. Gormley, 186 Mass. 168

The complainant can be asked whether the defendant is the father of the child. Is admissible as an accusation and not as leading to a conclusion. 

Com. v. Bird, 264 Mass. 485 

A complaint for nonsupport of illegitimate children. The defendant denied that he was the father, and it would seem that

CRIMINAL CASES 

this was a question involved and not that he refused or neglected to support them. Evidence was offered that the husband did not have access to her and that the defendant did have access. Where there is evidence of neglect or refusal by the defendant to support his children, such neglect or refusal is prima facie evidence that it was willful and without just cause and unreasonable. 

Com. v. Rosenblatt, 219 Mass. 197

The mother of the child was married and the mother was allowed to testify that her husband had deserted her for more than three years before the birth of the child, that she had not heard from him, and that the defendant was the father of her child. 

Her husband not being a party to the action, the testimony was admissible. 

Foss v. Foss, 12 Allen, 26 

If a man marries a woman knowing that she is quick with child and whom he himself has debauched, being induced to marry her by the assurance that the child was his, and not taking any further steps to ascertain its paternity, nor suspecting her of unchastity with any man other than himself, the court will not declare the marriage void, although it appeared that the child must have been in fact begotten by another man. 

Crehore v. Crehore, 97 Mass. 330

If a man marries a woman he knows is unchaste, having had sexual intercourse with her, the court will not declare the marriage void for the reason that she, on the day thereof, being at the time pregnant with a bastard child, of which he was not the father, assured him that she was not pregnant and he married her on the faith of that assurance. 

Dismissal of plea in bar. 

Com. v. Mitchell, 258 Mass. 89

At the trial in the municipal court of a complaint charging the defendant with being the father of an illegitimate child who was unborn at the time of making the complaint, the complainant and her witnesses were sworn and testified and it was suggested by the court and agreed to that the child had been adopted by the complainant and her husband by leave of the probate court. Whereupon with the consent of both parties the complaint was dismissed for want of prosecution. The defendant by thus consenting lost any rights which he otherwise might have had to plead to a second complaint charging him with nonsupport of the child.

CRIMINAL CASES 

291 That the dismissal of the first complaint was in legal effect an acquittal, or to contend that by the Acts of 1922, Chapter 432, he should not be held to answer to a second complaint. 

Com. v. Bressant, 126 Mass. 246

The dismissal of a complaint is not a dismissal and cannot be pleaded in bar. The effect of dismissing a complaint without a trial is like that of quashing or nol prossing the complaint. By neither of these is the defendant acquitted of the offense against him, but he is only exempted from liability on that complaint. 

Accusing other men evidence supplementary. 

Odewald v. Woodsum, 142 Mass. 512 

The complainant testified that the defendant begot her with child in October. The defendant could show that in September or October another man was in company with the complainant under suspicious circumstances. 

Eddy v. Gray, 4 Allen, 435

Evidence that the complainant was in the habit of associating with young men whose reputation for chastity was bad, is inadmissible. 

Sabins v. Jones, 119 Mass. 167

Evidence is not admissible that the complainant had intercourse with men other than the defendant more than twelve months before the birth of the child. 

Paul v. Paddiford, 16 Gray, 263

Evidence cannot be introduced of criminal intercourse ten months previous to birth or that her character or chastity was bad for two or three years before the birth of the child. 

Boyle v. Burnett, 9 Gray, 251 

The complainant cannot introduce evidence that five years previously she had had a child by the defendant which he admitted was his. 

Com. v. Moore, 3 Pick. 194

The general character as to chastity previous to her connection with the defendant and frequent intercourse with other persons is inadmissible. 

Ronan v. Dugan, 126 Mass. 176

Evidence that the complainant had intercourse with a man other than the defendant less than seven and one-half months

CRIMINAL CASES 

before the birth of the child is inadmissible as evidence that the birth was premature. 

Easdale v. Reynolds, 143 Mass. 126

Evidence of sexual intercourse with other men than the defendant six months prior to the begetting is inadmissible unless it can be shown that such intimacy continued down to the period of gestation. If one act occurred on or about the time of begetting, then acts of intimacy with other men are admissible. 

Ray v. Coffin, 123 Mass. 365

Under the old bastardy law the woman had to accuse at the time of travail, but under the new law this has been eliminated. 

IMMUNITY 

Com. v. St. John, 173 Mass. 566

A promise made by a probation officer without any authority from the district attorney to a person accused of a crime on the condition that he make a full disclosure cannot be pleaded in bar. 

IMPRISONMENT 

See under Escape. 

Com. v. Homer, 5 Met. 555 

Com. v. Briggs, 5 Met. 559

A defendant who was committed to jail on default of bail is not imprisoned. See General Laws, Chapter 268, Section 16, and amendment.

INCEST GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 272, SEC. 17 

Com. v. Ashey, 248 Mass. 259 A marriage of a man to the daughter of his half-sister is prohibited. The statute contemplates that the relations of the halfblood are the same as the whole blood. 

Sutton v. Warren, 10 Met. 451

A marriage valid where it is contracted is valid in this state, if not incestuous by the law of nature, or made void under the

CRIMINAL CASES 

293 laws, although it would be void by the laws of this state if contracted here. The intermarriage of a man and his mother's sister, though void by the laws of this state, is not incestuous by the law of nature and was not void by the law of England, though it was voidable by process in the ecclesiastical court. 

INNHOLDERS GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 140, SEC. 12 ACTS OF 1933, CHAP. 92 

Com. v. Lavaery, 188 Mass. 13

One who takes out a victualer's license in another's name and carries it on, not as agent, even if he honestly believed that he had the right to do so, is guilty, as no intent is necessary. “Assume" and "presume" are the same. 

Com. v. Meckel, 221 Mass. 70

Under Section 6 of Chapter 140 of the General Laws one who is licensed as a common victualer has no right to sell any kind of food on the Lord's day to be taken and carried away, where such sale is not a work of necessity or charity. He is not required to keep his place open on the Lord's day, but if one does so, he cannot sell food to be carried away. 

Com. v. Regan, 182 Mass. 22

The word "guest" signifies a relation determined by outward circumstances and not by inward intent. The decision as to the purchase of food as a mere subterfuge turns not on the ultimate motive, but on the external visible fact. An innholder must provide food for his guests, whether his guests' reasons for obtaining food in order to purchase liquor are honest or otherwise. 

Com. v. Graham, 176 Mass. 5

A common victualer is not a shop, and selling cigars on the 

Lord's day is a violation of law. 

Friends v. Childs Co., 231 Mass. 65

Definition of a common victualer. Cases on guarantee that the food is fit to eat. 

See under Common victualer, page 155

CRIMINAL CASES INSANITY GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 123 

Com. v. Rogers, 7 Met. 500 

Com. v. Taylor, 263 Mass. 356

A firm belief of a fact which is wholly imaginary and under that insane belief which would be justifiable if that fact existed, he is not responsible. An act done under an uncontrollable impulse, he is not responsible. A physician who attends a trial and hears the testimony is competent to decide the sanity of the defendant, though he never examined him. If the defendant could distinguish between right and wrong, he is guilty. 

Com. v. Spencer, 212 Mass. 438

One who is indicted for murder may be committed to the Psychopathic Hospital to determine his sanity. It is a temporary exercise and the person is held by the process of the court, not by the judge. The court keeps control. 

Sullivan v. Judges of Sup. Ct., 271 Mass. 435

Chapter 123, Section 99, applies to civil as well as criminal cases, and any person before the court can be examined mentally by the court under that section. In above case Sullivan was a plaintiff and a witness, and the fact that her mentality is called to the attention of the court by an adversee is not a bar. 

INSURANCE GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 175 

Com. v. Roswell, 173 Mass. 119

Where insurance is effected on property outside of this state the agent must be licensed. Acts of 1894, Chapter 522, Sections 87 and 88, are constitutional.

INDECENT EXPOSURE PUNISHABLE UNDER GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 279, SEC. 5 

Com. v. Haynes, 2 Gray, 72

The present form of the Boston Municipal Court, upheld. 

The pleader need not conclude to the common nuisance of all citizens, etc.

CRIMINAL CASES 

295 

INTERPRETERS 

Com. v. Patalone, 254 Mass. 69

An interpreter may testify that at a previous time when the defendant was called in another case, he appeared to have no difficulty in understanding the English language. 

Com. v. Vose, 157 Mass. 393

Two persons speaking different languages converse through a third person who understands only one language. The interpreter may testify on the complainant and the defendant; the conversation through the defendant affects the weight but not the competency of the evidence. 

Com. v. Lebowich, 235 Mass. 357

The presence of an interpreter in a grand jury room is legal. 

INTERSTATE 

Com. v. O'Neil, 233 Mass. 535 

A revised ordinance prohibiting driving a hackney carriage without a license. The defendant drove from one state into another. The ordinance was void as in the violation of the interstate commerce law. Query as to whether states have the right to do so.

JAMAICA GINGER 

Com. v. Sookey, 236 Mass. 448

The mere fact that certain liquids contain more than the legal per cent of alcohol does not make them intoxicating liquors within the meaning of Section 2 of Chapter 138 of the General 

Laws unless it is shown that it is a beverage; that is, liquor for drinking. 

Com. v. Lanides, 239 Mass. 103 

The defendant sold jamaica ginger. The defendant's intent in selling it is immaterial. If the article sold really is intoxicating liquor, it is illegal, even if sold as a medicine. The offense is not the intent, it is the act.

CRIMINAL CASES JUNK DEALER GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 140, SECS. 155 AND 156 

Com. v. Hood, 183 Mass. 196

One who buys old gold but does not sell it must have a license; he is a dealer in old metals within the statute. 

Com. v. Ringgold, 182 Mass. 308

A junk shop is a place where old metals, rope, and rags are bought and sold. One who buys new iron parts or pieces which are left from larger pieces to sell again is not a junk dealer. 

Com. v. Hubley, 172 Mass. 58

An ordinance for dealing in rags and junk is a valid one. 

Com. v. Silverman, 220 Mass. 552

One who sells junk on sixteen different occasions but who keeps no shop in which sales are made is found to be a dealer and must be licensed. In construing the word "dealer" in requiring persons to be licensed there is no difference between one who sells and one who buys.

JUDICIAL NOTICE 

Brodsky v. Fine, 263 Mass. 51

The courts do not take judicial notice of municipal ordinances. 

Com. v. Bezko, 280 Mass. 435

The courts now can take judicial notice that liquor is sold unlawfully and at times delivered to the buyer by men known as bootleggers. 

Com. v. Ing. of Springfield, 7 Mass. 9

The courts take judicial notice of towns created by law. If a complaint alleges that an offense was committed in the town of A without mentioning the county, it gives sufficient notice to the justice. 

Com. v. Wheeler, 162 Mass. 429

Where an indictment alleges that the crime was committed in W in said county and the context does not show which of two counties is referred to, the court cannot take judicial notice that there is a place named W in one of said counties, the indictment not stating that W was a town or a place within such county.

297 Declined.

JURISDICTION 

See also under respective headings of offenses. 

Com. v. Rice, 216 Mass. 480

The lower court can decline jurisdiction even after a finding of guilty, can recall and even sentence after the court has ordered the defendant to recognize to the superior court. 

Can be raised at any time. 

Com. v. Adler, 247 Mass. 580 

Com. v. Conlin, 184 Mass. 195

The jurisdiction of the court can be raised at any time. Even if the defendant does not raise the issue that a complaint sets forth no offense known to the law, it is the duty of the court to consider such a point of its own motion. 

Waiving jurisdiction. 

Com. v. Dailey, 12 Cush. 80

A defendant may waive any matter of form or substance excepting only what may relate to the jurisdiction of the court. 

Where jurisdiction of crime is uncertain. 

Com. v. Knowlton, 265 Mass. 382

The body of a woman was found on the road in Middlesex County. There was no evidence where the murder was com mitted. Under Section 57A of Chapter 277 of the General Laws the district attorney petitioned the court of his doubt where the crime was committed and the court gave Middlesex County the jurisdiction. Concurrent. 

Hopkins v. Com., 3 Met. 460 

Com. v. Prescott, 151 Mass. 60 

Concurrent jurisdiction fully discussed. 

Judicial district. 

Com. v. Clancy, 154 Mass. 128 "Within the judicial district of said court" is a sufficient allegation of the crime.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Hoar, 121 Mass. 375 "Within the judicial district of said court" shows that the crime was committed in a part of the county of which the court had jurisdiction. 

Jeffries v. Com., 12 Allen, 149

The phrase "of the City of Boston in the County of Suffolk" gives jurisdiction to the City of Boston. 

Com. v. Herbert, 264 Mass. 571

A complaint that alleges that the defendant "at Holyoke in the County of Hampden" charges a jurisdiction within the court. 

Land ceded to the United States. 

Com. v. Clary, 8 Mass. 72 

Opinion of the Justices, 1 Met. at 582

State courts have no jurisdiction over lands purchased by the United States. Civil and criminal process may be served therein by officers of the commonwealth, however, against any person charged with crimes without said property. 

Mitchell v. Tibbetts, 17 Pick. 298

Land ceded as Charlestown Navy Yard. Officers can serve warrants therein for crimes committed outside. No concurrent jurisdiction was reserved to proceed against offenses committed within the territory but only to give effect to such civil and criminal processes that the place might not be made a refuge and sanctuary for debtors and criminals. 

Waters of the United States. 

Com. v. Peters, 12 Met. 387

A person assaulting another on a boat three quarters of a mile from the navy yard and one third of a mile from a wharf in Boston Harbor is within the jurisdiction of the state court. 

Also true if he was on a vessel of the United States Navy if the vessel was within the territorial limits of the commonwealth. 

Com. v. Manchester, 152 Mass. 230 

Dunham v. Lamphere, 3 Gray, 268

The territorial jurisdiction of a nation over the adjacent seas, subject to the common right of navigation, extends by the law of nations to the distance of one marine league at least from the shore, and to bays wholly within the territory of the nation,

CRIMINAL CASES 

299 which do not exceed in width two marine leagues at the mouth; and within this jurisdiction is the right to control over fisheries in such waters, whether the fish are migratory and free swimming, or free moving, or attached to or embedded in the soil. 

Crimes committed on the high seas. 

Com. v. MacLoon, 101 Mass. 1

A citizen of another state or of a foreign country may be prosecuted for manslaughter of a person who dies within the commonwealth of injuries inflicted upon him by the defendant in a foreign merchant vessel upon the high seas. 

Venue of specific crimes-100 rods and 50 rods. 

Com. v. Gillon, 2 Allen, 505

An offense committed within 100 rods of the dividing line of two counties. A complaint in the county adjoining may allege that it was committed within the county in which the prosecution was instituted, discussion as to the complaint and indictment being synonymous. Either county has jurisdiction. 

Com. v. Matthews, 167 Mass. 173

If the crime is a local one, the place must be proved as laid. 

Boundary of 100 rods does not apply. 

Benedict v. Hunt, 1 Cush. 487

A in Connecticut commits an assault and battery and forcible detainer against B, who was on the Massachusetts side, and carried B back to Connecticut. The jurisdiction was in Massachusetts. 

Com. v. Costley, 118 Mass. 1

A conviction may be had in one county upon an indictment for murder if the pistol causing the murder was fired in an adjoining county more than 100 rods from the dividing line between the two counties and the death ensued in the county alleged in the indictment within 100 rods of the line. 

Com. v. Parker, 2 Pick. 550

If the cause of death happens in Suffolk County and death ensues in Middlesex County, an indictment may be found in the latter county. This is not repugnant to the declaration in the Constitution that in criminal prosecutions the verification of facts in the vicinity where they happen is one of the greatest securities of the life of the citizen.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Municipal judge sitting in Superior Court. 

Com. v. Leach, 246 Mass. 464

The judges of the district court when sitting in the Superior Court are not created judges of the Superior Court for the time being. They cannot be or become other than judges of the district court according to their respective commissions. The offenses which may be tried and the sentences which may be imposed by district judges sitting in the Superior Court are those within the original and concurrent jurisdiction of district courts. 

Corporation. 

Com. v. N. Y. C. Ry., 206 Mass. 417

Inferior courts have jurisdiction over corporations, and a summons served upon the proper officers of the corporation is the correct process for bringing the corporation into court. 

National bank, larceny from. 

Marsino v. Com., 252 Mass. 224

The sole jurisdiction of larceny from a national bank is in the state court. 

Com. v. Barry, 116 Mass. 1

An employee of a national bank may be convicted in the state court although he has committed an offense under the laws of the United States. 

Applied to counties. 

Com. v. Tolliver, 8 Gray, 386

Assault and battery committed in Boston was the allegation. 

Evidence introduced showed that it was committed in Chelsea. 

All that it was necessary to show was that it was committed at any place in the county and within the jurisdiction of the court. 

Question of age of the defendant. 

Com. v. Donohue, 126 Mass. 51

If the age of the defendant does not appear on the record in a criminal case, the objection that the court has no jurisdiction cannot be availed of by a motion to dismiss or arrest of judgment.

CRIMINAL CASES 

301 

Process returnable before another court. 

Com. v. Wolcott, 110 Mass. 67

If a justice of one court issues process returnable before another court having jurisdiction of the case, it is legal. The court of Central Berkshire issued a warrant returnable before the District Court of Northern Berkshire, which was proper. Bribery. 

Com. v. Hogan, 249 Mass. 555

A bribery was solicited in Plymouth, the money was paid in the County of Middlesex. The latter has jurisdiction of the offense of the acceptance of the bribe. The crime is one which may be committed in either of the acts charged. Conspiracy. 

Com. v. Saul, 260 Mass. 97

One participating in a conspiracy outside of this state whereby the owner of the goods was procured by another conspirator to deliver the goods to a common carrier in Boston for transportation to a company in Philadelphia controlled by the conspirators may be indicted and convicted of a conspiracy to steal, although he was not in the commonwealth at any time during the carrying on of the conspiracy. An indictment may be maintained in the district where the illegal design originated or where the overt act was committed. 

Counterfeit money. 

Com. v. Fuller, 8 Met. 313

The state courts have jurisdiction of having in possession counterfeit money with intent to pass. Larceny. 

Com. v. White, 123 Mass. 430

A thief who steals goods in another state and sells them in this state by an agent, not an accomplice to the larceny, may be indicted for the larceny here. The personal appearance of the thief is not always necessary to make him guilty of larceny. 

Com. v. Parker, 165 Mass. 526 

Com. v. Friedman, 188 Mass. 308

A defendant who receives property in one county with a felonious intent and carries it into another county may be indicted in the latter.

CRIMINAL CASES 

People v. Burke, 11 Wendall (N. Y.), 129

A foreigner who commits larceny in Europe and brings the property into New York may be tried and convicted in New York. 

State v. Bartlett, 11 Vermont, 650

One who steals property in Canada may be found guilty of larceny in the State of Vermont. 

Com. v. Uprichard, 3 Gray, 434

The bringing into this state by the thief of goods stolen in one of the British Provinces is not larceny in this state. 

Com. v. Holder, 9 Gray, 7 

Com. v. Rand, 7 Met. 475

A person who steals goods in Rhode Island and brings them here may be convicted here in this state. 

Com. v. Andrews, 2 Mass. 14

Goods stolen in one state and brought into Massachusetts jurisdiction may be tried here. Also the receiver of the same. 

Accessory before the fact. 

Com. v. Pettes, 114 Mass. 307

The defendant wrote letters to Boston furthering the uttering of the same in Boston. Jurisdiction of being an accessory before the fact here. One who procures a felony to be committed in a county of this state by means of letters written elsewhere can be convicted in that county of being an accessory before the fact. 

JURY 

Com. v. Gagle, 147 Mass. 576

After the jury had retired to consider its verdict, one juryman stayed in the jury room under guard while the other eleven went to supper. It was within the discretion of the court as to the propriety of the same. 

Florence Machine Co. v. Grover Baker Co., 110 Mass. 70

After the jury has announced that they have agreed on a verdict the judge may send them out again with further instructions before receiving the verdict.

CRIMINAL CASES 

303 

Com. v. Dorus, 108 Mass. 488

The jury were instructed that if they should agree on a verdict during adjournment, they might seal it and separate. They did agree and separated without reducing it to writing. On coming into court the judge directed them to reduce it to writing and return it without further deliberation and they did so. 

Verdict was invalid. 

Cook v. Castener, 9 Cush. 266

Partiality and misconduct of a juror in the juryroom cannot be shown by the testimony of the juror himself, or of the other jurors. 

Lawrence v. Stearns, 11 Pick. 501

The jury agrees and is allowed to depart and afterwards in coming into court one of the jurors dissents; the verdict cannot be recorded. 

Com. v. Smith, 9 Mass. 107

After an indictment had been received and filed by the court, no objection of irregularities in the empaneling of the jury can be received as a plea to such indictment. Quakers are eligible for jury duty. 

Com. v. Costello, 128 Mass. 88 

Com. v. Carrington, 116 Mass. 37

In a case of a felony, not a capital one, the jury may without the express consent of the defendant after signing and sealing the verdict, separate, and deliver their verdict orally upon the next coming in of the court. 

Com. v. Dow, 11 Gray, 316

On appeal the jury may take a copy of the complaint into the jury room, but it is bad practice to take the judgment; but this latter does not create any injury to the defendant. 

Com. v. Ricketson, 5 Met. 412

After a jury has retired and returns and makes an inquiry of the court as to a fact, it is within the discretionary power of the court to admit testimony respecting the matter of inquiry. 

Com. v. Roby, 12 Pick. 496

The court will permit cider to be furnished to the jurors but not spirituous liquors.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Eleven jurors. 

Com. v. Daley, 12 Cush. 80

By the consent of the defendant eleven jurors can render a verdict for a misdemeanor; if the attorney for the defendant consents, then it is with the consent of the defendant. 

Remarks and charges to the jury. 

Com. v. Cunningham, 104 Mass. 545

Where the district attorney in his argument stated that the defendant had previously been convicted of the same offense, this does not allow an exception for the defendant. 

Com. v. Harlow, 110 Mass. 411

It is erroneous to charge a failure to produce evidence which was in his power to produce. The court should have charged that failure to testify should not be taken against him, but the failure to produce other witnesses should be taken in mind. 

Com. v. Murphy, 10 Gray, 1

The attorney for the defendant cannot read decisions of other states to the jury. 

Com. v. Child, 10 Pick. 252

Formerly the judge had the right to express his own opinion to the jurors in regard to the weight of the evidence, but that practice is not so now. 

Com. v. Foran, 110 Mass. 179

If a witness for the purpose of betraying another induced him to commit a crime which he otherwise would not have committed, this testimony should be taken with great caution by the jury; but if he bought the liquor with an honest intention of helping the law, this would not apply. 

Law and facts. 

Com. v. Anthes, 5 Gray, 185

A jury cannot decide questions of law, and an act conferring such power is unconstitutional. 

Exemptions from serving on jury. 

Com. v. Walton, 17 Pick. 403

A member of the legislature while in session is entitled to be excused from serving on a jury. It is not within the exemptions, yet on general principles it is a sufficient reason for so doing.

CRIMINAL CASES 

305 

Com. v. Welosky, 276 Mass. 398 

Women are not eligible to serve as jurors.

Mistrial. 

Com. v. Cronin, 257 Mass. 535

The court has the power to declare a mistrial, and this is not a bar to subsequent prosecution. 

Waiver of jury trial. 

Com. v. Rowe, 257 Mass. 172

A defendant may waive any form or substance excepting only what may relate to the jurisdiction of the court (12 Cush. 

83). He can waive a trial by jury for a misdemeanor but not a felony. 

Child on the jury. 

Wassum v. Feeney, 121 Mass. 93

Although an infant is disqualified from serving on a jury, yet if his name is on the list of jurors returned and impaneled, his service on the jury does not entitle the party against whom a verdict has been rendered to have it set aside, although he was ignorant of the fact until after the verdict; the defendant had the opportunity of ascertaining the facts and he had the power of challenge.

Members of grand jury serving on a traverse jury. 

Com. v. Hussey, 13 Mass. 221

A new trial must be granted when it appears that two of the jurors which sat in the case had been members of the grand jury which found the indictment.

Reopening a case after impaneling. 

Com. v. Sholes, 13 Allen, 554

After a jury had been impaneled his attorney desired to file a motion to quash the indictment, and in order to do so the district attorney and the counsel for the defendant agreed to consider null and void all that previously had been done and if the motion was overruled the jury should be sworn again. This can be done.

CRIMINAL CASES JUSTICE Obstructing justice. 

Com. v. Perkins, 225 Mass. 80

It is a crime for a person to agree to obstruct justice by procuring a witness to absent himself from a trial, although the witness is not under a process to attend. 

Special justice. 

Com. v. Carney, 153 Mass. 444 

Com. v. Connor, 155 Mass. 134

In the judgment that is forwarded to the Superior Court on an appeal, the reason for the sitting of the special justice must be set out in the judgment, and unless this is done, a conviction cannot be maintained. 

Com. v. Coy, 157 Mass. 200

If the record in the Superior Court states that the special justice is authorized to sit, that fact need not be proved; there is a presumption that his acts are regular. 

Com. v. Hawkes, 123 Mass. 525

A special justice of a police court is a judge of any court in this commonwealth, within the 8th article of the Constitution, and he cannot hold that office and at the same time hold the office of a member of the legislature. 

Com. v. Brown, 158 Mass. 168

In a complaint addressed to the justice of the court the complaint and warrant need not allege that the special justice was sitting. 

Justice of the peace. 

Lawson v. Rowley, 185 Mass. 171

A justice of the peace cannot commit for contempt. 

Com. v. Foster, 1 Mass. 488

A justice of the peace has no authority to direct his warrant to a private person. 

Com. v. Leach, 1 Mass. 59 

Com. v. Burns, 14 Gray, 35

The common law duties of a justice of the peace discussed.

CRIMINAL CASES 

307 

Mead v. Bowker, 168 Mass. 234

Justice of the peace and trial justices are the same. 

Com. v. Vogel, 201 Mass. 261

A justice of the peace who charges more than the usual fee for performing a marriage is not guilty of extortion. The fee is fixed by statute. 

Com. v. Prescott, 151 Mass. 60

As to the Municipal Court concurrent with the Superior Court. 

Opinion of the Justices, 107 Mass. 604 

Formerly a woman could not be appointed a justice of the peace. Now she can be. 

Clement v. Bullens, 159 Mass. 193

On a complaint it need not be under seal. A justice has the same authority as a justice of the peace. The personally appearing is sufficient. "Before me" is not necessary. 

Opinion of Justices, 271 Mass. 575

A chief justice is the presiding officer and is different from an associate justice.

JUVENILE OFFENDERS 

Robinson v. Com., 242 Mass. 401

A juvenile offender may be bailed the same as an adult. 

Com. v. Dies, 248 Mass. 482

Under General Laws, Chapter 119, Section 52, a boy under the age of seventeen years may be indicted for a crime punishable for life or a term of years. 

Wassum v. Feeney, 121 Mass. 93 

As to an infant serving on a jury, see under Jury.

Nolan's Case, 122 Mass. 330 

The Acts of 1874, Chapter 258, Section 1, is unconstitutional, giving to trial justices the exclusive right to crimes against juvenile offenders and the sentencing to state prison. 

Com. v. Donohue, 126 Mass. 51

The defendant was sentenced in the lower court and appealed. The judgment sent to the Superior Court did not state

CRIMINAL CASES 

the age of the defendant. In the latter court the defendant moved to dismiss the complaint on the ground that the defendant was under the age of seventeen years at the time of his trial in the lower court. Motion was denied. The age of the defendant did not appear on the record and therefore there was not any legal reason for the dismissal of the complaint.

KIDNAPING ACTS OF 1934, CHAP. 1, AMENDING GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 265, SEC. 16 

Com. v. Turner, 3 Met. 19 

Form of complaint for. 

Com. v. Nickerson, 5 Allen, 518

The custody of a child was given to the father by the court. 

The agents of the mother seized the child in school and carried him away. Guilty. 

Com. v. Blodgett, 12 Met. 56

A state of civil war existed in Rhode Island. Soldiers from there came into Massachusetts, seized and arrested a man, and carried him back to Rhode Island. Guilty. 

Com. v. Bresnahan, 255 Mass. 144

One A and one B were divorced. The wife was given custody of the child, the father to see him at stated intervals. The defendant, at the instigation of the father, kidnaped the child on the street and took him to the father. The fact that the defendant did not know of the decree did not excuse him. Form for kidnaping. 

LABOR LAWS GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 149 

Mercantile establishment. 

Com. v. Hong, 261 Mass. 226

The defendant ran a Chinese restaurant. He engaged one A, who hired certain colored singers and dancers under 21 years of age, who sang and danced after 10 P.M. He could be found guilty of permitting the girls to work even if they were hired by an independent contractor.

CRIMINAL CASES 

309 

Com. v. Connor, 222 Mass. 299

A cashier in an enclosure was kept busy all the time. This comes within the law permitting women to work in a mercantile establishment exceeding 54 hours in one week. 

Merrymount Co. v. Edwards, 263 Mass. 282 

Tripp, v. Fay, 264 Mass. 516

A tea room is mercantile purposes. A club cafeteria, a barber, tailor and smoke shop are not used for mercantile or manufacturing purposes.

Pleading. 

Com. v. Dunn, 170 Mass. 140 

The pleader must allege in the complaint that certain wages are due to the employee; the statute is constitutional. 

Com. v. Mullen, 176 Mass. 132

As to the department making the complaint, any person can swear to a complaint even if he is not connected with the department. 

Com. v. Dyer, 128 Mass. 70

In a complaint for intimidating employees the pleader need not set out the means of intimidation. Form of complaint. 

Com. v. Osborne Mills, 130 Mass. 33

The pleader must use the words "did employ in laboring in a certain room." The phrase "did employ a woman in a certain room" is not sufficient. Must use the words of the statute. 

Com. v. Hong, 261 Mass. 226

Singing and dancing by girls held "work" under the statute prohibiting girls under the age of 21 years to work after certain hours.

Intimidation. 

Stewart v. Tagen, 207 Mass. 394

Four months after a strike had ended a team bearing placards saying "strike existed" was driven through the streets. The same can be enjoined in equity even if no damage ensues. 

Vegelahn v. Gunther, 167 Mass. 92

The maintaining of a patrol in front of a place of business is a part of a conspiracy to prevent business. Threats of personal injury can be prevented by equity. Writs may issue even if acts are criminal.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Sherry v. Perkins, 147 Mass. 212

Banners displayed in front of a person's premises with inscriptions calculated to injure his business and deter workmen from entering into or continuing in the employment constitute a nuisance which equity will restrain by an injunction. 

Com. v. Crane, 216 Mass. 356

The law that provides that one advertising for help must mention the fact that a strike or lockout or other labor disturbance exists is constitutional. The fact that it was advertised in only one newspaper, though the law says papers, the law is complied with. Employing. 

Com. v. Griffith, 204 Mass. 18

No child under 14 years of age, etc., is one of general application. A child in a show, receiving no compensation, is employed within the meaning of the statute. The statute does not conflict with Section 104 of Chapter 149 of the General Laws. 

One under engagement for theatrical work is "employed." 

Com. v. B. & M. Ry., 222 Mass. 206

The law providing that employees in and about railroad stations shall not be employed more than 9 working hours in ordinary healthy occupation is an unwarranted interference with the free dom of a contract. Acts of 1914, Chapter 746. 

Com. v. Hamilton Mfg., 120 Mass. 383

The statute which limits the hours of a woman in which she may be employed does not prevent her from working as many hours as she desires, but only limits her to the hours in the place where she is employed. 

Fining an employee. 

Com. v. Lancaster Mills, 212 Mass. 315

The law providing that an employer can fine an employee the deduction of his wages for imperfect work is not a fine under the law. Acts 1911, Chapter 584. 

Time notice. 

Com. v. Riley, 210 Mass. 387

Not posting a time notice under the Acts of 1919, Chapter 510, Section 48, is constitutional. The pleader need not

CRIMINAL CASES 

311 complain of the corporation; he can complain of the superintendent. Wages. 

Powers Case, 275 Mass. 515 "Tips" constitute a part of the average weekly wage of an employee. 

Ferry v. Kingsley Machine Co., 195 Mass. 548

The statute relating to the payment of wages to an employee on the next regular pay day does not invalidate a contract requiring a ten days' notice. Asportation.

LARCENY GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 266 

Com. v. Luckis, 99 Mass. 431

If the defendant's hand reached and seized the object in the pocket before he was arrested, and the position of the object was altered, it is larceny, and not an attempt. 

What constitutes larceny? 

Green v. Com., 111 Mass. 417 

Com. v. Adams, 7 Gray, 43 

Com. v. Kelley, 184 Mass. 320

To constitute there must be a taking and carrying away of personal property with an intent to steal it. A taking without carrying away or a carrying away without taking, is not larceny. 

Leaving the word "away" out of a complaint is fatal. See 111 

Mass. 417 and 254 Mass. 320. Not so now. 

Honest belief. 

Com. v. Stebbins, 8 Gray, 492 

Com. v. Hurd, 123, Mass. 438 

Com. v. Brisbois, 281 Mass. 125

One is not guilty of larceny if he took the money under an honest belief that he had a legal right to do so. If one was authorized to sell or honestly believed he had the right to take and sell property, the wrongful appropriation of the money and flight would not justify the jury in convicting. This is true although in fact no legal right existed.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Peakes, 231 Mass. 449

A treasurer of a corporation who obtains money of the corporation by means of forged receipts is guilty although the defendant thought he had the right to take it. 

Debtor and creditor relation. 

Com. v. Flynn, 167 Mass. 460

Property obtained by fraud but establishing the position of debtor and creditor, larceny cannot be maintained. 

Larceny of one's own property. 

Com. v. Brigham, 123 Mass. 248 

Com. v. Greene, 111 Mass. 392 

Whiteside v. Loney, 171 Mass. 431

The owner of attached goods may be convicted of larceny of the goods from the attaching officer. The sheriff had attached goods and the owner stole them. They can be alleged as the property of the officer. Evidence is admissible on the part of the owner that he intended and did leave enough goods to satisfy the claim of the attaching creditor. 

Date of the complaint. 

Com. v. Sego, 125 Mass. 210

It is not necessary to prove the time as alleged in the complaint for larceny as laid.

Pleading. 

Com. v. Gallagher, 16 Gray, 240

The pleader need not allege that coins to be current as money within this state. 

Com. v. Pratt, 132 Mass. 246 

Com. v. Rourke, 10 Cush. 397 Liquors purchased in violation of law and intended to be sold in violation of law are the subject of larceny. 

Com. v. O'Hare, 254 Mass. 564

Possession of an automobile need not be solely by the defendant, it is enough if it was in the joint possession of the defendant and one other person.

CRIMINAL CASES 

313 

Com. v. Cabot, 241 Mass. 131

While a thing stolen must be of some value to be the subject of larceny of it, or the receiver of it, it need not be an article having any special or market value. 

Green v. Com., 111 Mass. 417 

A complaint alleging "feloniously did take and steal." There was no averment that they were carried away. The defect is one of form and not of substance. A carrying away must be proved, however. 

Com. v. Grimes, 10 Gray, 470

The pleader need not allege the denomination of bills or coins of the amount and value in all of ... dollars. 

Com. v. Beamon, 8 Gray, 497

An indictment for the larceny of live animals. The pleader need not state that they were alive, because the law will presume them to be so unless the contrary be stated. But if, when stolen, the animals were dead, that fact must be stated, for as the law would otherwise presume them to be alive, the variance would be fatal. See Chapter 277, Section 27, of the

General Laws. 

Com. v. Simonds, 11 Gray, 306

When a corporation is averred in a complaint, the pleader must aver that it is incorporated by the laws of the commonwealth thusly: "the same being a corporation legally established." 

Com. v. Bannon, 254 Mass. 320 "Did steal a sum of money of the value of more than $100, the property of " is sufficient. ... 

Com. v. Williams, 2 Cush. 588

Some goods described rightly, some wrongly. General verdict, illegal ones can be stricken out and a verdict rendered on the ones properly pleaded. 

Com. v. Wyman, 8 Met. 247

Embezzlement. Evidence is admissible where money is obtained by embezzlement; the state can prove any facts of embezzlement committed within six months next after the day alleged in the complaint. An officer of a bank, not a clerk, servant, or agent, fails to come within the above and must be charged

CRIMINAL CASES 

specially, and each offense must be alleged in each count of the complaint. See Chapter 266, Section 53, of the General Laws. 

Com. v. Mead, 160 Mass. 319

The pleader need not allege that the money taken was actually the same as deposited. The money was taken from a bank and given to the defendant, who deposited the same in another bank. 

Com. v. Parker, 165 Mass. 526

Pleading the larceny of so many railroad tickets is sufficient. 

Com. v. Kelley, 184 Mass. 320

A complaint alleging that the defendant did steal moneys, the property of the estate of late of charges the embezzlement of the property. 

Com. v. Pratt, 137 Mass. 98 ...

A person who embezzles several articles of property at one time may be indicted and convicted of the embezzlement of each article separately. 

Com. v. Blanchette, 157 Mass. 486 

Purchasing goods under a false color, the form of complaint.

Possession of goods on this complaint will support a conviction of ownership, if alleged. 

Com. v. Parker, 117 Mass. 112

A complaint which alleges that the property was obtained by trick and other means is broader than the statute and is void. 

The complaint cannot be rejected as surplusage. 

Com. v. Brettun, 100 Mass. 206 

One promissory note of the value of $..... One piece of paper of one cent. Both can be pleaded in the same indictment. 

Need not allege for the payment of money. A piece of paper is a sufficient description. 

Com. v. Smith, 1 Mass. 245

In an indictment for the stealing of money the value must be averred. 

Com. v. Pray, 13 Pick. 359

An indictment for being a common thief is not sufficient, must be averred why.

CRIMINAL CASES 

315 

Deemed to be larceny. 

Com. v. Tenney, 97 Mass. 50 

Com. v. Simpson, 9 Met. 138

The phrase "deemed to be larceny" must also be alleged, and the offense must be substantially and formally set forth. Ownership. 

Com. v. Whitman, 121 Mass. 361

A messenger boy delivered a sum of money to an office, the one to whom it was addressed was out, but a man who was in the office signed for it, stating to the boy that he was not the one to whom the bundle was addressed. He signed a false name. 

The money can be alleged as the property of the man to whom it was addressed or the telegraph company delivering the same. 

Com. v. McLaughlin, 103 Mass. 435

In averring the larceny of money from the person and charging the ownership in the wife, who was carrying it into town for the purpose of purchasing goods, the property can be alleged as her property even though it is her husband's and he earned it. 

Com. v. Sullivan, 104 Mass. 552

An indictment for larceny may allege the property in the stolen goods to have been in the consignee, to whom they were in the course of transportation by a carrier when they were stolen, whether the carrier was designated or not. A delivery to the common carrier is a delivery to the consignee in the absence of any agreement to the contrary. 

Com. v. O'Hara, 10 Gray, 469

It is sufficient if either the special or general property be in the person alleged in the indictment. Goods stolen were in the possession of the pawnbroker and pledged to the pawnbroker and alleged to have been the property of the owner. 

Value of the property. 

Com. v. Riggs, 14 Gray, 376

If the articles alleged to have been stolen are of some value, it is sufficient. 

Com. v. Cabot, 241 Mass. 131

While a thing stolen must be of some value to be the subject of larceny or the receiving of it, it need not be an article having any special or market value.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Burke, 12 Allen, 182

The precise value of the article stolen is immaterial and need not be proved or found by the jury. If it is of some value, that suffices. 

Com. v. McKenney, 9 Gray, 114 

Com. v. Riggs, 14 Gray, 376

The state need not prove the precise value of the property alleged as long as the property is of some value. If the complaint alleges a value exceeding $100, the defendant may deny the value to be truly alleged and the jury may find and render a verdict of less than $100. But when the value does not exceed $100, the jury need not find any precise sum. 

Mingling funds with the defendant's own. 

Com. v. Libbey, 11 Met. 64

A person who is employed to collect bills for the proprietor of a newspaper and converts to his own use the money which he collects for them is not such an agent or servant as is intended to come within the statute of embezzlement; his having the right to mix the funds with his own takes him out of the statute. 

Commission merchants, auctioneers, and attorneys are not liable. 

Com. v. Stearns, 2 Met. 343

An auctioneer who receives money on the sale of his employer's goods and does not pay it over but misapplies it is not such an agent or servant as is intended under the embezzlement statute. Whether he receives the goods for sale in the usual mode, or receives them on agreement to pay a certain sum therefor, within a specified time after the sale. The money received by the auctioneer is his own and not the money of another. The defendant is liable for it as a debtor and not as a trustee. Having the right to mingle the money from the different sales with his own he cannot be convicted of embezzlement. 

Com. v. Smith, 129 Mass. 104

Salesman took orders, received payment for the same, and forwarded the same to the home office. He was to receive a commission and part of the profits in lieu of a salary. He had no property in the goods. He was an agent authorized to receive money on account of his employers and it is immaterial that his services were paid for, in whole or in part, by commissions or

CRIMINAL CASES 

317 profits on such sales. He had no authority either by his terms of the contract or the usage, nature, or otherwise of the business to mingle the same with his own funds. The relation of debtor and creditor did not exist. 

Com. v. Foster, 107 Mass. 221 

If one who is employed by the maker of a promissory note, not as a broker, but merely to sell it, receive the proceeds, and pay them over specifically to a third person, converts them to his own use, he is guilty of embezzlement. If he had employed him as a broker to negotiate the note in the due course of his business with authority derived from the nature of the business to mingle the proceeds with his own, his use of them would not be embezzlement.

Different methods of committing larceny. 

Com. v. Pappathanous, 257 Mass. 459

Stocks were never purchased. Complaint charged larceny. 

This could be made out by proof of embezzlement, obtaining property by means of false pretenses, larceny at common law, by fraud or trick, such that no possession of money had come to the defendant. 

Com. v. Althause, 207 Mass. 32

The fraud of obtaining goods by false pretenses without any intention to pay for the same might well be made a crime by the legislature, but it is not obtaining goods by means of false pretenses. 

Com. v. Lannan, 153 Mass. 287

If a person obtains possession of a large fund to a small part of which he is entitled, with the intent at the time to misappropriate the entire fund to his own use, and the owner does not part with the title, the crime is larceny. 

Com. v. Barry, 124 Mass. 325

If at the time of taking, the intent was to keep all, he being entitled to a part, larceny may be maintained. 

Com. v. Nichols, 257 Mass. 289

An officer of a bank willfully misapplies funds, credits, etc. 

Even if no loss ensues, there can be a conviction of larceny. 

No intent is necessary. Form of complaint under General Laws, Chapter 266, Section 53A.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Novick, 248 Mass. 317

An officer of a voluntary association can be convicted of larceny. He is not a partner. Now defined by statute. Chap ter 182, Section 1, of the General Laws. 

Com. v. Duncan, 250 Mass. 405

If a treasurer of a society loaned money without the consent of the directors or without authority, he is guilty of larceny. 

Com. v. Lee, 149 Mass. 179

A statement made by the defendant "he owns property all free and clear" and one acts on it at a loss, it is larceny, and applies if goods are to be delivered at a different time. 

Com. v. James, 18 Mass. 375 

Where a miller received barilla to grind, fraudulently retaining a part of it, returning a mixture, it was held to be larceny. 

Com. v. Ryan, 155 Mass. 523

The defendant, a bar tender, took money dropped in the cash register, which had been opened for another sale. He afterwards appropriated the money. If the defendant before placing money in the drawer intended to take it, it is embezzlement. 

Larceny in building. 

Com. v. Hartnett, 3 Gray, 450

As to being under the protection of the building, it is; otherwise not. 

Com. v. Doherty, 127 Mass. 20 The defendant was charged with embezzlement of a bank book from A, which had been delivered to the defendant by A with directions that the defendant should only have the custody of it and should hold it until demanded by A. In order to convict the defendant of the crime charged it was necessary to prove actual and legal possession was parted with it by the owner and vested in the defendant, who became bailee of the property for safe keeping to be delivered back to the owner when demanded. 

Com. v. Marsino, 252 Mass. 224

Larceny from a national bank is not punishable under the federal statutes, and the sole jurisdiction is in the state courts. 

In the federal court the defendant was sentenced for aiding and abetting misapplication of funds, in the state courts the defendant was charged with larceny.

CRIMINAL CASES 

319 

Com. v. Morrison, 252 Mass. 116

It is not necessary to prove that the false pretenses were the only inducement to the larceny, it is enough if they were the decisive influence. False pretenses may be made in any of the ways in which ideas may be communicated. 

Com. v. Call, 21 Pick. 515

A false pretense may be made to an agent who communicated it to his principal and who is influenced by it to act; may well be laid as a false pretense to the principal, so obtaining money from an agent who pays it by direction to his principal may be laid as obtaining it from the principal. 

Russell on Crimes, 2d vol., 88-89

Actual possession though wrongful is sufficient to support an indictment for larceny, as if the goods of A were stolen by B and afterwards stolen from B by C. An indictment against C may allege the title to be either in A or B. 

Holst v. Stewart, 161 Mass. 516

One bargaining with another must use reasonable diligence to discover for him facts obvious to an ordinary observer, of which the means of knowledge are equally available to both parties. 

Com. v. Hutchins, 232 Mass. 285

An attorney held out certain sums from a person; held to be larceny. He was acting for one person and not for several, as distinguished from that decided in Com. v. Stearns and Com. v. 

Libby. He took fees; also agent not an attorney at law. Collection of cases as to embezzlement and larceny. 

Dennie v. Smith, 129 Mass. 143

Refusal of a constable to return property to the owner on the determination of the suit in the latter's favor is a breach of the condition of his bond, and he is liable in conversion. 

Com. v. McDuffy, 126 Mass. 467

A person who by fraudulent representation obtains from another a sum of money which is no more than is rightfully due him from the latter cannot be convicted of obtaining money by means of false pretenses. 

Com. v. Levine, 280 Mass. 83

The defendant as treasurer of a corporation ordered in conjunction with another person certain paint to paint the building

CRIMINAL CASES 

of the corporation. In fact the treasurer bought the goods for himself by false pretenses. The seller intended to pass title to the corporation but could not as the purchase was not bona fide. 

When the goods were delivered the defendants got a title and were guilty of larceny. 

Com. v. Snow, 284 Mass. 426 

Com. v. Moore, 166 Mass. 513

A gave Snow, the treasurer of a company, money to invest in the purchase of certain property in Kansas; the defendant 

Snow gave A a receipt stating "for investment only." The depositor A did not give Snow the right to mingle the money so deposited with the company funds and use the money for any other purpose in the business. A trust relationship was established between the parties and the relationship of debtor and creditor did not exist or was established. 

Com. v. O'Keeffe, 121 Mass. 59

If money is given by donors to the defendant (who is chairman of a committee to collect funds) to deliver to the treasurer of a society for the purpose of paying some expenses incurred, and he, the defendant, did not deliver the same but converted it to his own use, he is guilty of larceny. 

Com. v. Chesley, 107 Mass. 223

The defendant pretended that he had and held a lease on a building which he could assign. Guilty. 

Com. v. Hussey, 111 Mass. 432

A gave B some promissory notes to hold for him in trust and confidence with the direction that B should return them to A on demand. B retained them and was found guilty of embezzlement. There need not be any demand. The complaint charged the sum of $60, but the jury could find the embezzlement of any part. 

Com. v. Whitcomb, 107 Mass. 486

Obtaining property by fraud and deceit, even if it is parted with for a charitable purpose, is larceny. 

Com. v. Mason, 105 Mass. 163

The defendant took a horse while it was trespassing on his premises with the intent to conceal it until the owner offered a reward, then return it and claim the reward, which makes it larceny.

CRIMINAL CASES 

321 

Rex v. Phillips, 2 E. P. C. 662

The defendant took some horses and rode them to a distant village and there left them. He did not intend to go after them or make further use of them. This is not larceny. There was not any intent to deprive the owner of them but merely to convert them to his own use. 

Com. v. Shepard, 1 Allen, 575

Larceny by a bank officer under Section 52 of Chapter 266 of the General Laws. Altering the deposit slips after taking the money for deposit. 

Com. v. Tuckerman, 10 Gray, 173

The treasurer of a corporation receives money and deposits the same to his credit as treasurer. He afterwards draws it out. 

May be found guilty of embezzlement and there need not be any demand made. 

Com. v. Hays, 14 Gray, 62

Money paid out by mistake to a person who was withdrawing the same from a bank and he converts it to his own use. He is not guilty of embezzlement. 

Com. v. Stebbins, 8 Gray, 492 

Com. v. Hurd, 123 Mass. 438

Taking money to appropriate it to the payment of a debt due the taker from the person from whom it is taken is sufficient evidence of a conversion to the taker's own use, and constitutes larceny. 

Com. v. White, 11 Cush. 483

The defendant took a horse and wagon from a stable and drove it to a nearby town, and while on the way he changed horses. He then drove to another county where he sold them. 

Although he took the property and intended to return it, he can be convicted of larceny in the county where he committed the trespass. Discussion of larceny and trespass. 

Com. v. Wilgus, 4 Pick. 177

A naked lie may not be sufficient to maintain an indictment for false pretenses, for it is not the policy of the state to punish criminally every wrong which is committed. 

Com. v. Chace, 9 Pick. 15

Doves are animals "ferae naturae" and cannot be the subject of larceny except when they are in the custody of the owner,

CRIMINAL CASES 

as, for example, in the dove cote. The defendant shot them and used them for food. 

Com. v. Strain, 10 Met. 521

Representing a watch to be a gold one and it is not, money being obtained thereby is larceny. 

Vincent v. Cornell, 13 Pick. 294

A delivered property to B to be kept until a day certain and to be taken care of by B; and on or before that day certain B had the right to purchase it. Before that day B sold the property to C, an innocent purchaser. No larceny. 

Com. v. Levenson, 250 Mass. 440

An attorney at law represented to his client that he, the defendant, required more money than already had been paid in order for the attorney to make restitution. The statement was false. The court held that this statement was not future promise or future expectation but an assertion of a past or present fact. 

Com. v. Parmenter, 121 Mass. 354

A representation that a promissory note is genuine and is secured by a real estate mortgage is a false pretense. 

Jurisdiction. See also under Jurisdiction. 

Com. v. Barry, 125 Mass. 390

A, in accordance with plans of B, switched checks on a trunk at Worcester. B in New York took the trunk and rifled it. A could be convicted of larceny, Worcester asportation not being necessary. 

Com. v. Uprichard, 3 Gray, 434

The bringing of property into this state by a thief of goods stolen in the British Provinces is not larceny in this state. 

Com. v. Holder, 9 Gray, 7 

Com. v. Rand, 7 Met. 475 A person who steals property in Rhode Island and brings the same into this state can be tried in this state. 

Com. v. Friedman, 188 Mass. 308

Under an indictment under Section 30 of Chapter 266 of the General Laws a defendant can be prosecuted only in the county in which he had possession of the goods alleged to have been stolen.

CRIMINAL CASES 

323 Com. v. Wood, 142 Mass. 459 

The defendant received a draft in Minnesota sent there from Berlin, Massachusetts, at the request of the defendant. The false pretenses were made in Berlin. When the draft was placed in the mail it passed out of the control of the complainant into the control of the defendant. The postmaster was the agent of the defendant. The crime was committed in Berlin, where the draft was sent from. 

Com. v. Taylor, 105 Mass. 172
