The defendant ordered merchandise in Boston to be shipped into Vermont. The person to whom it was shipped in Vermont was fictitious, the defendant himself receiving them. A delivery to the carrier is a delivery to the consignee and must apply in this case. The defendant having actually received them himself, there was not any delivery to a person in Vermont, and the law will not apply that the pretenses having been made in Massachusetts and the goods delivered in Vermont, the jurisdiction is in Vermont. 

Com. v. Lavery, 101 Mass. 207 

Com. v. Marsino, 252 Mass. 224

The complaint alleged a larceny in the building in the County of Suffolk. The evidence disclosed that the larceny was committed in Chelsea. A verdict could be rendered of simple larceny. Larceny is not local in its nature and the particular place is not material if it is in the same county. 

Com. v. Felton, 101 Mass. 204

The courts of the United States are vested with the exclusive jurisdiction of all crimes that are made punishable by the Acts of Congress, except where Congress makes other provisions; therefore if a cashier of a National Bank embezzles money, the state courts have no jurisdiction. 

Com. v. Tenney, 97 Mass. 50

The laws of the United States provide a penalty for embezzling funds of the bank but not for the funds of the individuals deposited there, so the state courts would have jurisdiction. 

Recent possession of stolen property. 

Com. v. Randall, 119 Mass. 107 

Com. v. McGorty, 114 Mass. 299

Recent possession of property like the kind which had escaped from the hands of the owner, where no sufficient explanation is given by the defendant, warrants a conviction.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Deegan, 138 Mass. 182

If property is stolen and is found in the possession of a person who bought it of another two weeks after the larceny, the latter may be convicted. 

Verdict and evidence. 

Com. v. Sessions, 169 Mass. 329

It is not necessary to prove the whole amount taken as alleged in the complaint. 

Com. v. Rowe, 257 Mass. 172

Property of a value of $100 is a felony; property under $100 is a misdemeanor. 

Com. v. O'Hare, 254 Mass. 564

The possession of a stolen automobile need not be solely by the defendant; it is enough if it was in the possession of the defendant and one other. 

Com. v. Gallagher, 126 Mass. 54

On a verdict for larceny it is not necessary to prove that the amount of the property stolen is not more than the alleged amount. 

Com. v. Gavin, 121 Mass. 54

An indictment alleging the larceny of a number of bottles of whisky is not sustained by proof that the defendant drew the liquor from the casks into bottles which he took with him for that purpose. 

Com. v. Johnson, 250 Mass. 320

The complaint charged that the goods stolen were the property of G. F. Richardson; the evidence disclosed that G. F. Richardson had no ownership in the property. Held that this was not a variance that could be cured by Section 35 of Chapter 277 of the General Laws but a variance going to the merits of the accusation. 

Com. v. Hill, 14 Mass. 207 

One who is deaf and dumb may be convicted of larceny. 

Bushman v. Com., 138 Mass. 507

A complaint charged different counts of larceny from different persons. The court is not bound to assume that the larcenies were one and the same offense although alleged to have been committed on the same day.

CRIMINAL CASES 

325 

Com. v. Jenks, 138 Mass. 484

On an indictment against several defendants one may be convicted. 

Com. v. Collins, 138 Mass. 483

The larceny of several articles of the same kind, it is not necessary to state the number, nor to allege the value of each article. Can allege articles of an aggregate value, and divers promissory (meaning bank bills) of the amount and value in all, etc. 

Com. v. Jones, 136 Mass. 173

If two persons are jointly indicted for larceny and one of them pleads guilty, the other cannot be convicted without proof of joint action with the former in the larceny of which he has pleaded guilty. 

Com. v. Harley, 7 Met. 467

It is not necessary in order to convict that joint defendants or either of them obtained goods on their own account or derived or expected to derive personally any pecuniary benefit therefrom. 

Com. v. Lucas, 2 Allen, 170

One who, in pursuance of a preconceived plan devised by himself, remains downstairs in his own house while his confederate above secretly takes the pants of a lodger, brings them down the stairs, and there delivers the money taken to him, may be convicted as a principal. 

Com. v. Glassman, 253 Mass. 65

Seven counts each of a different date, a general verdict is good. The defendant requested an acquittal that the dates set forth and the evidence were not in accord. The time is not essential, and a verdict of guilty was good. 

Felton v. Gregory, 130 Mass. 176

It is not duress for a thief to make restitution of the stolen money for the purpose of mitigating his sentence. 

Com. v. Pierce, 130 Mass. 31

An allegation charging two persons jointly with obtaining a loan of money by means of false pretenses is a descriptive allegation and is not supported by proof of a loan to one of the persons only.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Cooper, 130 Mass. 285

It is no defense to an indictment for larceny of money that it was intrusted to the defendant for an illegal purpose. 

Com. v. Dyer, 243 Mass. 472

A member of a corporation is indictable. 

Com. v. Clair, 7 Allen, 525

A person indicted for stealing shoes cannot be convicted of proof that he stole boots. 

Com. v. Collins, 12 Allen, 181

A left a watch in a jeweler's shop to be repaired. B called and impersonated A, paid the repair charges, and took the watch. 

Guilty of larceny. 

Com. v. O'Malley, 97 Mass. 584

The defendant took a sum of money for the purpose of deducting a portion which was to be a loan and return the residue. 

He refused on demand to return it. Guilty. 

Fitzgerald v. Jordan, 11 Allen, 128

If an officer serving a warrant for larceny takes from the defendant the goods alleged to have been stolen and the defendant is acquitted, it is the duty of the officer to return the goods, and if the person arrested owns them and demands the return, the refusal to do so subjects the officer to an action of conversion. 

Com. v. Young, 9 Gray, 5

A mechanic receiving material to be made into shoes at his own shop is not the servant or agent of the person furnishing the leather. 

Com. v. Sanders, 14 Gray, 394

Property alleged as the property of A. A subsequently died. 

His statements and declarations are inadmissible. He was not a party to the record. 

Com. v. O'Connell, 274 Mass. 315

Under Section 57 of Chapter 266 of the General Laws (form for) one appointed by the probate court to act who intentionally acted in violation of the court in the distribution of funds is guilty of larceny.

CRIMINAL CASES 

327 

Com. v. Eastman, 2 Gray, 76

It is no grounds for arresting judgment on an indictment for stealing articles of personal property that some of the articles were insufficiently described. 

Com. v. Cunningham, 104 Mass. 545

Where the only evidence of the larceny is the identity of the prisoner with the guilty party, although no witnesses will swear positively to the identity, the jury will be justified in convicting. 

Com. v. Stone, 4 Met. 43

He who receives a bill of a closed bank not knowing it to be such and on a representation made to him that it is worth its nominal amount does not take it at his own risk. 

Com. v. Merrifield, 4 Met. 468

Printed sheets were given to a publisher to be bound and sewn. These, after they were given to the publisher, were stolen. 

The indictment alleged larceny of the printed sheets, which was wrong. The defendant should have been charged with larceny of the completed, that is to say, larceny of the books. 

Cheat at common law. 

Com. v. Warren, 6 Mass. 72

Where one by lying without using false weights, measures or tokens defrauds another of his property, the injury is a civil one. The defendant said his name was A and desired to purchase goods and gave a note in payment. No crime. 

Memorandum. 

Com. v. Steinberg, 265 Mass. 45

Property was sent from Boston to Northampton on memorandum, the agreement being consigned on memo to remain the property of the complainant, goods not selected to be returned within five days. It was sufficient to make out the offense if the fraudulent representation was a decisive although not the sole influence operating on the mind of the complainant to induce him to give up the articles. If the defendant has sent the complainant notes for the property then larceny would not have been committed. If the defendant notified the complainant that he intended to keep the property before he went into bankruptcy, then the title passed to the defendant, even if he did not send the notes and there would not have been any larceny.

CRIMINAL CASES LAW OF THE ROAD GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 89 

Broult v. Hanson, 158 Mass. 17

A law of the road which requires that when persons meet on a bridge or road, traveling in vehicles, they must pass to the right, does not apply where there were no persons in the vehicle. 

Norris v. Saxton, 158 Mass. 46

A law of the road does not apply to a highway formed by the junction of two streets which cross each other diagonally. 

Hays v. B. E. Ry., 256 Mass. 493

Section 7 of Chapter 89 of the General Laws. A fire apparatus returning from a fire has not the right of way. They are not going to a fire or responding to an alarm. 

Foster v. Curtis, 213 Mass. 79

Automobiles and street cars are vehicles and automobiles must pass street cars going in the same direction by driving to the left. 

Fales v. Dearbon, 1 Pick. 345

One driving must keep to the right. He may use the middle or the left side when he does not interfere with the rights of others. 

Com. v. Gammons, 23 Pick. 201

The law of the road extends to all roads whether de jure or de facto whether they are appropriated by public authority or by general license of the owners thereof expressed or implied, and such owners themselves while using their land as a road must conform to this law. 

Com. v. Allen, 11 Met. 403 The law of the road requires that travelers in carriages who meet in a road are required seasonably to drive the carriage to the right of the middle of the traveled part of the road, and they cannot avoid conviction by seasonably turning to the right of the wrought part of the road, though they leave sufficient room for the travelers whom they meet to pass with convenience and safety in the use of ordinary care and skill.

CRIMINAL CASES 

329 

Lovejoy v. Dolan, 10 Cush. 495

The rule requiring travelers meeting each other "seasonably to drive to the right" does not apply when one vehicle is passing along one street, and another is turning into said street from a cross street. 

Garrigan v. Berry, 12 Allen, 84

There being no statute regulating the manner in which persons shall drive when they meet at the junction of two streets, the rule of the common law applies, and each person must use reasonable care to avoid a collision, adapted to the place and circumstance. Now changed by statute, Chapter 89, Section 8. 

Reynolds v. Hanrahan, 100 Mass. 313

The court cannot say as a matter of law that a one-armed man driving a carriage in the day time down a hillside at a rate of five miles per hour on the right of a traveled road four rods wide was negligent. 

McGourty v. DeMarco, 200 Mass. 57

The driver of an automobile who goes to the right of a street car which has stopped to let off passengers instead of to the left as required by law is negligent. 

LEWDNESS AND LEWD AND LASCIVIOUS COHABITATION GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 272, SECS. 16 AND 53 

Open and gross lewdness. 

Com. v. Catlin, 1 Mass. 8

An indictment for open and gross lewdness will not be sustained by evidence of lewdness and lascivious behavior in secret. 

Both parties participated in the act without objection, and both parties intended to be private and attempted to conceal. 

Com. v. Wardell, 128 Mass. 52 

Com. v. Cummings, 273 Mass. 229

Indictment for open and gross lewdness is supported by proof that a man intentionally and indecently exposed his person without necessity or reasonable excuse therefor in the house of another to a girl eleven years old. Indecent exposure being a common-law misdemeanor must amount to a common nuisance committed in

CRIMINAL CASES 

a public place and seen by more than one person. The word "lewdness" at common law means open and public indecency but in the criminal statutes in this state it has a broader sense and means "the irregular indulgence of lust whether public or private." In this particular statute the word "open" qualifies the intention of the perpetrator of the act. It need not be public in the sense of being in a public place or in the presence of many people. It is enough if it be an intentional act of lewd exposure offensive to one or more persons present, and a complaint is maintained by exposure of the person to an eleven-year-old girl. 

Com. v. Cummings, 273 Mass. 229

The defendant and another man were in the main toilet adjacent to a public waiting room of a railroad station, indulging in indecent acts which they stopped when anyone entered and resumed when the people left. This is open and gross lewdness in the sense of the statute. 

Single instance of. 

Com. v. O'Brien, 179 Mass. 533 

Single instances of lewdness under Section 53 of Chapter 272 

of the General Laws will not support a conviction. The complaint was for being a lewd, wanton and lascivious person in speech and behavior. On the other hand, if the one instance shows the person to be a person of that type, then the complaint is good and the person is guilty. The defendant can offer evidence that he went into the room with an innocent intent. 

Com. v. Calef, 10 Mass. 153 A complaint for lewd and lascivious cohabitation. A single act of criminal intercourse will not support the above complaint. By cohabitation it must be understood a dwelling or living together and not a single or transient unlawful intercourse. See in connection with 4 Cush. 49. Pleading. 

Com. v. Parker, 4 Allen, 313

This complaint was pleaded continuando. The court said, "The offense or misconduct which is thus made punishable consists in being a person of the character and behavior described. 

He is none the less so that this bad character is the result of his conduct in both of these particulars. There is therefore in the complaint neither uncertainty nor duplicity; for in a prosecution

CRIMINAL CASES 

331 against an offender the offense may be set forth according to a fact. And as it is alleged that the person was a character described, both by reason of the improper and unlawful manner in which she conducted herself and of the words and speeches which she uttered, she is charged with only a single offense. This offense, if it may consist in a single act or speech, may also consist in a series of acts or speeches, or of misconduct in this way continued through a considerable period of time. When the offense consists in a continued or protracted violation of the law, it is sufficient and proper to allege it in the words and language of the statute. 

It is difficult, and perhaps impossible, to describe the matter charged against the defendant in more definite or intelligible terms than it is in the complaint." 

Com. v. O'Brien, 179 Mass. 533

Being a lewd, wanton and lascivious person in speech and behavior is pleaded continuando. This does not set forth two offenses. One instance of a single act will be sufficient. 

Com. v. O'Brien, 179 Mass. 534

The complaint was pleaded continuando. The court said, "The statute punishes being a certain kind of person, not doing a certain overt act, as was recognized by the words which we have quoted from the complaint. See Com. v. Parker, ubi supra.

When, then, as in this case, the proof is confined to conduct on a single occasion, it does not sustain the complaint unless the conduct fairly warrants an inference that the defendant is a person of the kind described. 

Com. v. Bradley, 2 Cush. 553 

Com. v. Hunt, 4 Cush. 49

In an indictment for lewd cohabitation if the pleader avers that A and B were married on a day certain and afterwards he married another woman and lived in this commonwealth with the last named, it is sufficient notwithstanding there is no allegation of time. 

Com. v. Dill, 159 Mass. 61

A complaint alleged that A and B abided together instead of associated together and is not defective. 

Evidence. 

Com. v. Munson, 127 Mass. 459

The mere fact of cohabitation under an honest though mistaken belief that they are married will not support an indictment

CRIMINAL CASES 

of lewd and lascivious cohabitation if it is not under such circumstances as to create a public scandal. 

Newport v. Boothbay, 9 Mass. 414

From a long-continued cohabitation of a man and woman as husband and wife, a marriage may be presumed by the jury. 

Com. v. Porter, 237 Mass. 1 The keeping of a house wherein are committed indecent and unnatural acts by men with others of the same sex is an offense punishable under Chapter 139, Sections 4 and 5, of the General Laws. An acquittal of being a lewd person is not a bar to the above offense.

LIBEL COMMON LAW. JURISDICTION UNDER GEN. Laws, Pleading. 

CHAP. 218, Sec. 26 

Com. v. Varney, 10 Cush. 402

An indictment for libel on B need not describe B as of any profession, occupation or place of residence. An indictment charging the defendant published a libel on the 21st of the month is supported by publication on the 19th. If the indictment alleged the libel was published in a paper dated the 21st the libel would have to be proved as laid. 

Com. v. Harmon, 2 Gray, 289

It is not necessary to set forth the whole of the paper in which the libelous matter is contained, but that part may be selected which is most offensive. 

Com. v. Park, 1 Gray, 553

An indictment which charges the defendant with publishing a libel is supported by evidence that he procured another person to publish it. 

Com. v. Child, 13 Pick. 198

In libeling a member of the legislature, the pleader must aver intrinsic facts and not so pleaded is void. 

Com. v. Szliaskys, 254 Mass. 424

If the words alleged to be libelous are not in themselves so, pleader must plead by means of introduction, if it is new matter, by way of innuendo, if it is only a matter of explanation.

CRIMINAL CASES 

333 

Jurisdiction. 

Com. v. Blanding, 3 Pick. 304

A libel was published in a paper printed in Rhode Island, but the number containing the libel was received and circulated in 

Massachusetts. The jurisdiction was in Massachusetts. Publish ing a correct account of a judicial proceeding, but with comments and insinuations tending to asperse a man's character, is libelous. Privileged. 

Cowley v. Pulsifer, 137 Mass. 392

A publication in a paper of a petition for the removal of an attorney at law which had not been presented to the court or entered on the docket is not privileged. 

McLaughlin v. Cowley, 127 Mass. 316

A defamatory statement contained in the declaration in a libel by counsel is not pertinent or material to the issue, is not privileged, and in an action against the counsel he cannot justify by showing his belief that it was true. 

Com. v. Pratt, 208 Mass. 316

False statements of fact in regard to the conduct of a public man are not privileged, merely because the manner in which he performs his public duties is a matter of general interest, while comment and fair criticism are permissible even though they are disparaging. 

Laing v. Mutten, 185 Mass. 233

Statements made to a magistrate having jurisdiction to receive them are privileged even if uttered maliciously. 

Truth of statements. 

Com. v. Damon, 136 Mass. 441

Form for. When a daily paper published a letter written by a contributor. Truth is a complete defense, unless express malice in the publication is shown; malice in the proper sense of hatred and ill will. The uttering or publishing of similar reports are admissible in evidence. 

Com. v. Clapp, 4 Mass. 163 

The truth of words is no justification in criminal proceedings in libel. May be shown to negative malice. May show purpose of publicity. The publicity of truth concerning the character of a

CRIMINAL CASES 

public officer and relating to his qualifications for office made with intent to inform the public is not libelous. Evidence. 

Morrill v. Crawford, 278 Mass. 250

Words charging immoral character or conduct do not import violation of a criminal law. 

Riceman v. Union Co., 278 Mass. 149 

Charging one with being a bootlegger is libelous. 

Thompson v. Boston Pub. Co., 285 Mass. 344

The filing of a complaint in a district court and the issuance of a warrant bearing its seal and the teste of the court were judicial proceedings, within the rule of law making privileged, fair, and accurate reports of judicial proceedings when made without express malice even though the warrant was issued by the clerk in the absence of the judge, the clerk to that intent exercising judicial powers. 

Com. v. Enwright, 259 Mass. 152 

In slander (11 Cush. 241) witness may testify to what he understood the defendant to mean and to whom certain signs and gestures apply. In above case a picture was part of the indictment and witnesses who had known the person libeled could testify that the picture suggested the libeled person. 

Finnish So. v. Socialistic Co., 238 Mass. 345

A corporation can recover for libel of its character and reputation. 

Com. v. Morgan, 107 Mass. 199

A paper published that A was a state cop being drunk. Held libelous. 

Odiarne v. Bacon, 6 Cush. 185 

The terms "cheat" and "swindler" are not actionable unless spoken of in relation to business. 

Com. v. Snelling, 15 Pick. 337

When the innuendo is useless and unnecessary, it may be rejected as surplusage. Evidence of a general bad character of the persons against whom the charges are made is inadmissible. 

Miller v. Butler, 6 Cush. 71 

If two persons participate in the composition of a letter and one of them mails it, both are guilty of publishing. The publica-

CRIMINAL CASES 

335 tion need not import a criminal charge, it is sufficient if it tends to subject the party to ridicule and contempt. 

Com. v. Chapman, 3 Met. 68

The publication of a false and malicious libel has always by the common law been an offense punishable by indictment. 

LICENSES Guilty knowledge. 

Com. v. Barnes, 138 Mass. 511

One holding a license must at his peril keep within the terms of it. 

Com. v. Uhrig, 138 Mass. 492

If the servant of the licensee sells to a person whom the licensee does not authorize such a sale to, the licensee may be convicted of an illegal sale, although neither he nor his servant intended to violate the license, and the sale was made in ignorance of the fact that the purchaser was one of a class to which no sale could legally be made. 

Com. v. Kelley, 140 Mass. 441

A license granted to a licensee makes him responsible for certain things connected with his license. If it forbids him to do or permits certain things to be done, he alone is guilty. 

Com. v. Merriam, 136 Mass. 433

One held a license to sell liquor. The license through an error on the part of the board issuing it, was defective, and the defendant could not justify under it.

Burden of proof. 

Com. v. Towle, 138 Mass. 490

In a criminal prosecution in which the defendant relies for his justification upon any license, appointment or authority, he is required to prove the same.

Revocation of. 

Com. v. Kinsley, 138 Mass. 578 

Rafferty v. Police Com., 259 Mass. 145

The police commissioner can revoke or suspend a license for hackney carriages without a hearing.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Bus license. 

Com. v. Theberge, 231 Mass. 386

An automobile jitney so called, passing through a town and not stopping, and not taking on or letting off passengers, must take out a license in that town. 

Evidence. 

Com. v. McGann, 213 Mass. 213

A keeper of a theatrical building outside of Boston must obtain two licenses, one from the city and one from the Chief of 

Police. The chief may suspend at will. 

Com. v. Kimball, 7 Met. 304

The records of a licensing board that a certain person is not licensed, and the same is prima facie evidence that he was so licensed. 

Com. v. Bolkom, 3 Pick. 281

A book is admissible showing that A was licensed. The same need not set forth the fact that a license was granted. 

Gasoline pump. 

Com. v. Willcott, 259 Mass. 406

The selling and keeping of gasoline and the storing of the same in pumps are the keeping and selling from a structure. 

The pumps were in a yard apart from the structure. If a condition in a license granted for the sale of gasoline is not fulfilled within a reasonable time after it is granted, the license afforded the defendant no grounds for protection. 

Engaging in the business of. See under Business. 

Admission ticket. 

Burton v. Scherpf, 1 Allen, 133

An admission to a concert is a revocable license to the purchaser to enter the building where it is being presented and to attend the performance, and if revoked before the performance has commenced and before the holder has taken his seat, if he remains in the building after notice of revocation and refuses to depart upon request, he becomes a trespasser and may be removed by force necessary for that purpose.

CRIMINAL CASES 

337 

Authority to issue-approval. 

Leroy v. Worcester St. Ry., 287 Mass. 1

Where the mayor of a city gave general instructions to the clerk of the licensing authority thereof to endorse the mayor's approval by a rubber stamp upon all licenses for a bus issued by the licensing authority unless the clerk was otherwise directed by the mayor; and several years later the clerk without further instruction by the mayor, so endorsed the mayor's approval upon a license so issued, there being no personal consideration thereof by the mayor, such license is invalid for want of proper approval by him. 

Hackney carriage. 

Burrell v. Taxi Co., 287 Mass. 108

The police commissioner has the power and right to grant licenses in front of buildings even against the abutting owners who have a fee in the street and who protest against the issuance.

LOBSTERS GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 130 

Com. v. Young, 165 Mass. 396

Lobsters shipped from Nova Scotia and repacked in this state, but not sold or offered for sale here, warrants a finding of possession in this state. The Acts of 1887, Chapter 314, applies to lobsters caught out of the state. 

Com. v. Savage, 155 Mass. 278

Having in possession illegal lobsters is an offense no matter when they were taken. The possession is the offense. Returning them to the water applies only when they are caught. 

Dunn v. Lowe, 203 Mass. 516

The right to search for lobsters under Section 102 of Chapter 130 of the General Laws does not give the right to open the sack of the person carrying the same. Pleading.

LORD'S DAY VIOLATIONS GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 136 

Com. v. Shannihan, 145 Mass. 99 

A complaint for keeping open shop on the Lord's day need not negative the exceptions.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Nagle, 117 Mass. 142

The words "except works of necessity or charity" apply only to the clause "or does any manner of labor business or work," and has no reference whatever to either of the other branches of the statute. 

Com. v. Wright, 12 Allen, 187

Form of complaint for keeping open shop. The complaint charged that the defendant at a place and on a day named, that day being the Lord's day and between the midnight preceding and the midnight succeeding the said day, did keep open his shop there situated and numbered ...... in for the purpose of doing business therein, the same not being then and there works of necessity or charity; is good. It does not charge two offenses. 

. 

Com. v. Harrison, 11 Gray, 308

Proof need not be confined to the particular day of the month alleged; if the acts were done on the Lord's day it is sufficient. 

Com. v. Collins, 2 Cush. 556

In an indictment for keeping open shop there must be an averment that it was kept open for the transaction of business, or for some other unlawful act. 

Com. v. Dextra, 143 Mass. 28 

If a complaint alleges that the defendant "kept open his shop on the Lord's day, the same not being a work of necessity or charity," the latter phrase can be rejected as surplusage. 

Shop is openwhen. 

Com. v. Kirshen, 194 Mass. 151 

Com. v. Harrison, 11 Gray, 310 A shop is kept open if all who please can enter, although the entrance is closed. 

Com. v. Lynch, 8 Gray, 384

Keeping open a shop is supported by evidence that persons entered, purchased, and the door was unlatched. Persons purchased three cents' worth of brandy. Necessity. 

Donovan v. McCarthy, 155 Mass. 543

Acts of necessity and charity fully discussed. See 118 Mass. 195.

CRIMINAL CASES 

339 

Com. v. White, 190 Mass. 578 

Gathering cranberries on the Lord's day is not an act of necessity. 

Stone v. Graves, 145 Mass. 353

It cannot be ruled as a matter of law that the work of shaving a man aged and infirm in his own house on the Lord's day is not a work of necessity or charity. Baker. 

Com. v. McCarthy, 244 Mass. 484

Bakers cannot deliver bread on Sundays to licensed victualers between 11 and 12 o'clock; it is not a necessity under Section 5 of Chapter 136 of the General Laws. The delivery of cream, milk, and papers is a necessity. 

Com. v. Crowley, 145 Mass. 430 

One who keeps open a store on the Lord's day to sell bread that he purchased elsewhere is not a baker and does not come within the exception. 

Mail carrying. 

Com. v. Knox, 6 Mass. 76

The carrying of mail on the Lord's day is not a violation of the statute when done alone, but if done in connection with other business, such as collecting passengers and blowing a horn, it then becomes a violation. Old law not good now. 

Selling land. 

Com. v. De Voe, 159 Mass. 101

A person who induces others to visit on the Lord's day a tract of land which he has for sale, and there shows him a plan of it, and talks with him about its value, price, and location, is doing business on the Lord's day. 

Illegal business. 

Com. v. Trickey, 13 Allen, 559

One may be convicted of a violation of the Lord's day although the business done by him is illegal, as unlawfully selling liquor.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Selling cigars. 

Com. v. Graham, 176 Mass. 5

If one of the purposes for which a common victualer keeps open his shop on the Lord's day is the sale of cigars, the keeping of that place open for that purpose is unlawful. 

Com. v. Goldsmith, 176 Mass. 104 

Com. v. Marzynski, 149 Mass. 68

Cigars and tobacco are not drugs so as to come within the meaning of the statute. 

Religious belief. 

Com. v. Has, 122 Mass. 40

Although he conscientiously believes that the seventh day of the week ought to be observed as the Sabbath and actually refrains from labor on that day, he can be convicted of keeping open his shop on the Lord's day for the purpose of doing business. 

Com. v. Kirshen, 194 Mass. 151

It is no defense to a complaint for keeping open a shop on the Lord's day that the defendant conscientiously believes that the seventh day should be observed as the Sabbath. One is guilty of keeping open a shop if workmen are allowed in and out, the doors being locked and the public excluded. Proceeds. 

Com. v. Alexander, 185 Mass. 551

A concert on the Lord's day by a church society. Proceeds mean the net proceeds after the payment of expenses. Ownership. 

Com. v. Doyle, 144 Mass. 363

The one in charge of the store is guilty of the violation for keeping open a shop on the Lord's day even if he is only hired. 

Keeping and doing business. 

Com. v. Dextra, 143 Mass. 28 

Keeping open a shop on the Lord's day is one offense, the doing of business is another and distinct offense.

CRIMINAL CASES 

341 

LOST PROPERTY GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 134, SEC. 1 

Com. v. Titus, 116 Mass. 42 

Felton v. Gregory, 130 Mass. 176

The finder of lost goods may lawfully take them into his possession and if he does so without any felonious intent at the time, a subsequent conversion of them to his own use, by whatever intent that conversion is accomplished, will not constitute larceny. But if at the time of the first taking them into his possession he has a felonious intent to appropriate them to his own use and to deprive the owner of them, and then knows or has reasonable means of knowing or ascertaining by marks on the goods or otherwise who the owner is, he then is guilty of larceny. 

McAvoy v. Medina, 11 Allen, 548

A stranger in a shop who first sees a pocketbook which had been accidentally left by another person upon a table there, is not authorized to take and hold possession of it as against the shopkeeper. 

Slot machines.

LOTTERY GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 271 

Com. v. McClintock, 257 Mass. 431 

Com. v. Ward, 281 Mass. 119

A mechanical device whereby, having inserted a nickel in a slot and pulling a lever, the operator receives a package of candy and either, no metal disks in number equaling a certain figure which has been displayed in a recess on the machine before the coin was deposited, whereupon either a new figure or the word "no" appears in the recess is a gambling device under Section 7 of Chapter 271 of the General Laws. It is an appeal to the gambling desire and is unlawful. Pleading. 

Com. v. Harris, 13 Allen, 534

A lottery for money sufficiently indicates a lottery in which prizes are to be drawn for money. Setting up and promoting is not bad for duplicity.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Clapp, 5 Pick. 41 

Under the section for advertising lottery tickets, the pleader need not allege that the lottery was within the commonwealth, or specify the lottery. 

Com. v. Sheedy, 159 Mass. 55

A complaint which alleges "did dispose of one suit of clothes of the value of $155" is defective. The pleader must allege to whom the suit was disposed of to, and if not known, it must so be pleaded. 

Com. v. Sullivan, 146 Mass. 142 "Did set up and promote a certain lottery for money a more particular description of the same being to the said complainant unknown," is good. 

Com. v. Horton, 2 Gray, 69

The pleader need not aver the name of the lottery or describe the articles disposed of, or state their value, or the names of the persons who received them as prizes. He must, however. allege that the article was of some value. Evidence. 

Com. v. Harris, 13 Allen, 534

A man may be guilty of setting up a lottery without having the tickets in his possession for sale, and he may sell a lottery ticket or have it in his possession with intent to sell, without the slightest participation in the setting up or the promotion of the lottery. He may have purchased or received a ticket from a stranger, and if he offers to sell it afterwards, he would be guilty of the offense. Proof of the possession and the sale of a lottery ticket and nothing more would not sustain the charge of promoting a lottery. 

Com. v. Emerson, 165 Mass. 146

The defendant sold tobacco to a customer who also at the same time selected a photograph. This is not an inducement to purchase and a violation of the statute. If the sale was made and the purchaser did not know what the inducement was, then it is a violation. 

Com. v. Brockway, 150 Mass. 322

The defendant in the house of another raffled a horse which was won by B but never delivered to B. Delivery is not essential to a violation.

CRIMINAL CASES 

343 

Com. v. Gorman, 164 Mass. 549

The offense of having policy slips in one's possession can be committed at any time. The defendant was arrested for being an idle and disorderly person, and the slips were found in his possession. The mere fact that he was not arrested for lottery does not render them incompetent. 

Com. v. Wright, 137 Mass. 250

The player chose a number and played for it. The conductor of the game then drew an envelope from a box which contained a slip with a number on it. If the number chosen was found among those on the slip, the person who chose it received a multiple of the sum less or greater according to the odds. This is not a wager or a bet. 

Com. v. Thatcher, 97 Mass. 583

A ticket which purports to entitle the holder to whatever prize may be drawn in a scheme called a prize concert, prizes of money, one half of the tickets being blanks, is a lottery. 

Com. v. Dearbon, 15 Mass. 125

A lottery was legal and conducted under the protection of the law in A.D. 1818. 

Com. v. Dana, 2 Met. 329

Books kept in relation to a lottery are "materials for a lottery." 

Com. v. McKay, 177 Mass. 345

Both in the popular and legal sense the word "lottery" signifies a scheme for the distribution of prizes by chance. Setting up and promoting a lottery is broad enough to include for money, and if after a trial and conviction a motion is filed, it is then too late. 

Com. v. Coleman, 184 Mass. 198

The provisions of Section 8 of Chapter 271 of the General 

Laws that "whoever knowingly suffers money or other property to be raffled for" in a house, shop, or building controlled by him "or to be won there by throwing or using dice or by any other game of chance, shall be punished," etc., describes only one offense. Need not state the name of person winning the raffle. 

Whitman v. Fournier, 233 Mass. 154

A voting contest so called, conducted by a merchant, whereby to purchasers of merchandise at his store he gave blank votes

CRIMINAL CASES 

which they could use for themselves or deliver to others, and to the persons whose names received the highest number of votes he gave prizes, all persons concerned knowing in advance that the contestants receiving the most votes at the end of the contest would be entitled to prizes and the only way to procure votes was by purchasing goods of the merchant or by procuring them for other contestants, is not a lottery nor within the prohibitions of Section 7 of Chapter 271 of the General Laws. 

Kemp v. Hammond Hotels, 226 Mass. 409

The hotel was held liable when a guest lost his money in one of the rooms by betting on a horse race. The room was fitted up with a blackboard and other paraphernalia for betting which the manager of the hotel, the court found, should have been cognizant of. The plaintiff did not have to prove knowledge by personal observation by the owner, occupant, or tenant, but if he was in a position to have known he should have known. 

Hotel was liable. The knowledge of the manager was imputed to the owner. 

Com. v. Sisson, 178 Mass. 578

Trading stamp law. Delivering trading stamps with articles sold for cash in accordance with previous announcement entitling the purchaser to select and receive one of a number of articles exhibited at the store of an independent corporation issuing the stamps, is not a violation, as there is no gambling element in the transaction.

MAIL CARRIER 

Tepper v. B. E. Ry., 192 Mass. 46

A trolley car carrying mail has no more rights to the exclusive right of the street than any other car and cannot violate the law. 

All the protection that it is entitled to is that no one shall hinder the passage of the car. 

Com. v. Closson, 229 Mass. 329

A mail wagon and carrier are amenable to the laws of this commonwealth. The facilities for the transportation of the mails confer no extraordinary rights to mail carriers to use the ways as they please, nor do they necessarily or impliedly do away with the powers of supervision and control inherent in the state.

CRIMINAL CASES 

345 

Com. v. Knox, 6 Mass. 76

Carrying mail on the Lord's day is not a violation. The 

United States has the right to contract and make the laws, and a state cannot pass laws to conflict.

MALICIOUS DESTRUCTION OF PERSONAL PROPERTY GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 266, SEC. 127 

Com. v. Williams, 110 Mass. 401

In pleading the offense of willful and malicious injury to a building the pleader must aver and use the word "wantonly." 

The injury must be done out of cruel hostility or revenge. 

Com. v. Byard, 200 Mass. 175

Wantonly. An act done heedlessly without regard to propriety demanded by circumstances and in reckless disregard of others with a total absence of care is wantonly. If one acts honestly and believes he had the authority to act is not wantonly. 

A tree warden who cut off the branches of a tree in moving a building is guilty of malicious mischief. 

Com. v. Hosman, 257 Mass. 379

The defendant was indicted for accessory before the fact to malicious injury to personal property, to wit, two automobiles in value exceeding $100. The defendant was advised by the owner of a car that the defendant was operating that if he was stopped by an officer to crash through them and not to stop. 

Such a spirit of ill will and hostility is willful and malicious. 

The value of the property was not introduced during the trial that it did not exceed $15 (General Laws, Chapter 266, Section 127). As a matter of common experience the jury could find that each one of the automobiles was worth more than $100. 

Com. v. Walden, 3 Cush. 558

In order to convict for malicious mischief the jury must be satisfied that the injury was done either out of a spirit of wanton cruelty or wicked revenge. 

Com. v. Soule, 2 Met. 21

An indictment which avers that "who ever injures and destroys" is sustained by cutting five feet off from a wire cable

CRIMINAL CASES 

rendering it useless. If property was destroyed for the purposes of which it is designed and rendered unfit it is "destroyed." 

Com. v. Sullivan, 107 Mass. 218

An indictment for willfully and maliciously destroying certain goods can be maintained without an averment that they were destroyed, although evidence shows that they were so injured as to be unfit for further use and were worthless. 

Com. v. Falvey, 108 Mass. 304

Poisoning hens may be averred as willful and malicious destruction of the personal property of another and may be averred as fourteen hens of a collective value. The pleader need not specify the means. That it was done willfully and maliciously is sufficient without a more distinctive averment of guilty knowledge on the part of the defendant. 

Com. v. Dougherty, 6 Gray, 349

A complaint for maliciously destroying a shrub on the land of another must aver an unlawful entry by the defendant on the land. 

Com. v. Galatta, 228 Mass. 308

Under a violation of Section 127 of Chapter 266 the pleader need not allege that the destruction was in a manner and by means not particularly mentioned in said chapter. 

Com. v. Cox, 7 Allen, 577

A complaint which alleges that the defendant did willfully and maliciously injure an omnibus of the value of five hundred dollars by willfully and maliciously driving the pole of a horse railroad car at, against, and through a panel of the said omnibus, by means of which the said panel was broken in pieces, and the said omnibus was otherwise greatly injured, contains a sufficient averment of the value of the property injured, and that the panel was part of the omnibus and no averment of the amount of the injury is necessary. A verdict of guilty which specially finds the damage alleged in the indictment to have been less than $15 is a good conviction. 

Com. v. Bean, 11 Cush. 414

In a complaint for maliciously destroying glass the pleader must aver that the glass is part of the building. An allegation that it was in a certain building is not sufficient.

CRIMINAL CASES 

347 

MALICIOUS PROSECUTION 

Keefe v. Hart, 213 Mass. 476

The retention of a prisoner for one and one-half hours before presenting the facts to a magistrate when he was arrested as a suspicious person was an unlawful retention. 

Graves v. Dawson, 133 Mass. 419

An action for malicious prosecution can be maintained when against the defendant's objection and remonstrance the case was nol prossed. 

Wax v. McGrath, 255 Mass. 340

The defendant, a police officer, arrested the plaintiff on the suspicion of committing a felony. After his arrest and before arraignment he was found to be innocent and was requested to sign a release waiving all rights. He refused to do so but after being incarcerated for several hours and there being illness at home, he signed the waiver under protest. The waiver was no good and he can recover. 

Shea v. Sullivan, 261 Mass. 255

A minor arrested on a train at the request of a passenger. The defendant was retained in custody for two days and he then signed a release. A minor can waive a right. 

Desmond v. Fawcett, 226 Mass. 100

A collection of cases on malicious prosecution. 

MARRIAGE 

See also under Husband and wife, Adultery, Evidence, Polygamy. 

Merrick v. Betts, 214 Mass. 223

At common law before the abolition of slavery slaves actually held in slavery in a slave state could not contract a lawful marriage and have legitimate children. 

Finer v. Steuer, 255 Mass. 611

A ceremony of marriage performed in a foreign jurisdiction and followed by cohabitation is presumed to have been performed according to law, and one attacking it has the burden of proving its invalidity.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Bergeron v. Bergeron, 287 Mass. 524

If parties live in Massachusetts and one of them obtains a Mexican divorce, the divorce is void in this state. 

MASTER AND SERVANT 

See under Lord's day, Evidence and Alcoholic beverages. "MAY" AND "SHALL" 

Com. v. Mekelburg, 235 Mass. 383 "May" means must or shall only in cases where the public interest is governed or where the public or third persons have a claim de jure, that the power should be exercised. 

Attleboro v. Com. of Corp., 257 Mass. 43

While the word "may" ordinarily construed is permissive, in construing statutes applicable to public officers in connection with a duty to be performed it is construed as "must" or "shall" if that is the legislative intent. 

Town of Milton v. Cook, 244 Mass. 93

In its ordinary significance "shall" is imperative and not permissive, although it may have the latter meaning when required by the context. 

Rea v. Ald. of Everett, 217 Mass. 427 "Shall," although not a word of inflexible signification in its popular and common meaning, is imperative and mandatory. 

Dascalakis v. Com., 244 Mass. 568

The word "may" relative to the granting by the Superior Court of a new trial in criminal cases is used in the permissive sense and not in the imperative sense. 

Phillips v. Fadden, 125 Mass. 198

In the statute relative to the arrests for drunkenness the word "may" leaves to the sound judgment of the officer any discretion whether to arrest or not, but it does not change the purpose of the arrest when made. The words "shall" and "may" are not infrequently equivalent terms. 

Bay State Ry. v. Woburn, 232 Mass. 201

The word "shall" in its ordinary signification is mandatory and not permissive. It is to be given that meaning unless by the

CRIMINAL CASES 

349 context it appears that a different one was intended or is necessary to effectuate the chief purpose of the statute. Even "may" in connection with a duty to be observed is used in the imperative rather than a cautionary sense in instances where the intent of the lawgivers seems to require it. 

Com. v. King, 202 Mass. 379

In the language of the statute relating to a bill of particulars the original statute used the word "shall." This later was changed to "may" and this cannot be treated without significance how-. ever either word might be construed if it stood alone. 

MEDICINE GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 112 

Com. v. Jewelle, 199 Mass. 558

The defendant used a vitalizer and a ray machine and prescribed medicine. One can be guilty of practicing medicine if he practices the healing art and the art of science which relates to the prevention, cure, or the alleviation of disease without dealing out medicine. 

Dugdale v. Bd. of Medicine, 270 Mass. 65

A physician who falsely procured money from a patient regarding her disease was delicensed for misconduct. The decision of the Board of Medicine is final unless the court finds that the evidence was clearly wrong. 

Com. v. Porn, 195 Mass. 443

Whether the practicing of midwifery is a violation of unlawful practice of medicine is a question of fact. If facts agreed on a question of law. cine. 

Com. v. Zimmerman, 221 Mass. 184

Chiropractic is a violation of the lawful practice of medi- 

Com. v. Lindsey, 223 Mass. 392

Judgment reached through clairvoyance is unlawful practice of medicine. 

Com. v. Houtenbrink, 235 Mass. 320 

Optometry is unlawful practice of medicine by using bill heads and the title of Dr.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. De Lon, 219 Mass. 217

The prescribing of medicine in accordance with the revelations of occult force while in a trance is unlawful practice of medicine. 

Joy Gouy Shong v. Joe Shee, 254 Mass. 366

To prescribe and furnish medicine is to practice medicine. 

Com. v. St. Pierre, 175 Mass. 48

Holding oneself out as an eye doctor is within the law if the defendant did not prescribe. If he sold medicines receiving payment therefor and gave advice gratuitously as the use to be made of them he did not violate the law. If he was paid for his services, the situation would be different. 

Com. v. Jameson, 221 Mass. 184

One who calls himself a chiropractic and is not licensed is guilty.

Davis v. Bd. of Medicine, 251 Mass. 283 

Assisting an unregistered physician is sufficient for revocation. 

Com. v. Dragon, 239 Mass. 549

There can be an unlawful practicing of medicine without the dealing of drugs or the prescribing of drugs or other substances as medicine. The words "practicing or practitioner of medicine," and "practice of medicine" are held to include the practice of surgery and the art of setting fractured bones, although no prescriptions are given. 

Com. v. Runge, 231 Mass. 598

A complaint which charges a continuing offense between the dates of March 3, 1915, and August 3, 1915, will not include any acts done prior to the first date.

Pleading. 

MILK LAW VIOLATIONS GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 94, SECS. 12-63 

Com. v. Luscomb, 130 Mass. 42

In an indictment charging the defendant with having in his custody and possession with intent to sell "one pint of adulterated milk to which water had been added" the allegation is descriptive

CRIMINAL CASES 

351 and is not supported by proof that the milk in question was adulterated by adding water to pure milk. 

Com. v. Graustein 209 Mass. 38

The complaint alleged that the defendant had in his possession milk to which a foreign substance had been added, a further description of which was unknown to the complainant. The chemist testified that the foreign substance was cow dung. The fact that the complainant did not know of this when he took oath to the complaint does not bar the evidence of the chemist.

The word "added" does not mean that it must become soluble in milk; any substance foreign no matter in what form it is added. 

Com. v. Holt, 146 Mass. 38

In cases where the milk has not been taken under the provisions of the statute evidence is determined by the common law, and any person with sufficient skill to analyze may testify. 

In this case the defendant had no sample of the milk analyzed. 

Com. v. Proctor, 165 Mass. 38

The inspector must give a sample of the milk to the producer or the one from whom he takes it. 

Com. v. Coleman, 157 Mass. 460

The collector took a sample of the milk and did not disclose the fact that he was an inspector and did not give the defendant a sample. Held that this evidence was admissible. He was not acting under Acts of 1886, Chapter 318. 

Com. v. Lockardt, 144 Mass. 132

Handling the samples of milk and the sealing of the same by the inspector must be complied with. It is a condition precedent and must be complied with. 

Com. v. Carter, 132 Mass. 12

The law which allows all inspectors to enter all cars used in the conveyance of milk to take samples for analysis is constitutional. 

Servant and agent. 

Com. v. Proctor, 165 Mass. 38

The master is liable for the acts of his servant if he has the control over him.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Warren, 160 Mass. 533

The owner may be convicted whose servant in the course of employment makes an inadvertent sale of milk. If the inspector pays a round sum for his meal in which the milk is a part, it is a sale of the milk. 

Com. v. Vieth, 155 Mass. 442

Milk sold by the servant of a hotel keeper in the ordinary course of employment; the master is guilty, although not present and not knowing and not consenting to the sale. Adulterated. 

Com. v. Bowers, 140 Mass. 483

Having adulterated milk in possession. The state need not prove the manner in which it was adulterated below the standard, if intent is to sell pure milk and not as skimmed milk. 

Com. v. Foss, 205 Mass. 384 

Com. v. Wheeler, 205 Mass. 384

Nutritious milk which is fit for food but falls below the standard is adulterated within the meaning of the statute. 

Com. v. Evans, 132 Mass. 11

A person may be convicted of selling adulterated milk without an allegation or proof that he knew it was adulterated. Intent. 

Com. v. Graustein, 209 Mass. 38 

Com. v. Evans, 132 Mass. 11 

A criminal intent is not essential in order to convict. Corporations. 

Com. v. Graustein, 209 Mass. 38 

The word "whoever" includes corporations. Evidence. 

Com. v. Rowell, 146 Mass. 128

A driver of a wagon with the name and license number on it containing milk cans from one of which adulterated milk was taken, is in possession of the same. The complaint averred that 

A and G were copartners in trade, did have in possession, etc. 

The state need not prove the copartnership; it does not go to the essence of the complaint and may be rejected as surplusage.

CRIMINAL CASES 

353 

Com. v. Wetherbee, 153 Mass. 159 "Milk to which a foreign substance had been added" applies to skim milk. 

Com. v. Gordon, 159 Mass. 8

The word "milk" from which no part of the cream had been removed is a general name and in a sense broad enough to include cream. 

Com. v. W. H. Co., 209 Mass. 30 

Concentrated milk is not within the statute. "Milk," as used in the General Laws, Chapter 94, means whole or natural milk of the cow. 

Com. v. Elm Farm, 221 Mass. 68

Milk as used in the statute covers cream. The statute is violated by having in possession milk to which water had been added by cream separated by centrifugal process. 

Com. v. Graustein, 209 Mass. 38 

See under Pleading. 

Com. v. Titcomb, 229 Mass. 14

Chapter 94, Section 37, of the General Laws is constitutional. 

Not class legislation; does not apply to dealers who are not producers. 

Com. v. Smith, 141 Mass. 135

Inspectors of milk cannot appoint an agent who shall have the right in the absence of the inspector and without his personal direction and control to take without consent milk from a wagon. Pleading. 

Com. v. Keenan, 139 Mass. 193

The defendant had in his custody and possession a certain quantity, to wit, one pint of adulterated milk, to wit, milk then and there containing less than 13 per cent of milk solids with intent then and there unlawfully to sell the same; is sufficient. 

Com. v. O'Donnell, 1 Allen, 593

A complaint which alleges that A, being a dealer in milk and being recorded as such in the books of the inspector of milk, sold adulterated milk does not sufficiently allege that he was recorded in the books of the inspector as a dealer in milk.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Farren, 9 Allen, 489

It is not necessary in a complaint to allege that the milk was cow's milk. An indictment which charges the defendant sold a certain quantity of adulterated milk to which a large quantity, that is to say, four quarts, of water had been added is not bad for duplicity. 

Com. v. Nichols, 10 Allen, 199

An indictment which alleges that the defendant did unlawfully keep, offer for sale, and sell adulterated milk charges but one offense. 

Com. v. Schaffner, 146 Mass. 512

An allegation that the defendant had in his possession milk to which a foreign substance had been added, to wit, annatto coloring matter, with intent unlawfully to sell the same, is sufficient without naming the quantity.

MISLEADING ADVERTISEMENT GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 266, SEC. 91 

Com. v. Reilly, 248 Mass. 1

Form of complaint held good and not in violation of the Constitution.

MISDEMEANOR 

Com. v. Sherman, 191 Mass. 439

There are no degrees of guilt in a misdemeanor. All are principals. In the commission of a misdemeanor there are no degrees of guilt, all who participate in it are principals and may be charged as such. All criminal cases which are not felonies are misdemeanors. There are no third classes consisting of nuisances. 

MISNOMER 

Com. v. Seeley, 167 Mass. 163

Persons known and commonly called by name in the indictment as their true name or full name is not a misnomer. 

Com. v. Warren, 167 Mass. 53

A child called Etta Keats in the indictment whose true name was Eva Keats. It is a question for the jury as to a variance.

CRIMINAL CASES 

355 

Com. v. Williams, 161 Mass. 442

The name was Preston Q. Sweet as pleaded in the indictment. 

His true name was O. Preston Sweet. Evidence was offered that he was as well known by one name as the other. Not a variance.

Com. v. Jacobs, 152 Mass. 276 

Incorporated as the "Warren Social Club." If it is known as the "Warren Club" there is not any variance. 

Com. v. Perkins, 18 Mass. 388

Junior is no part of the name. An indictment charging Thomas 

Perkins Jr. was bad in that it should have charged Thomas Hopkins Perkins. 

Com. v. Stone, 103 Mass. 421

The assaulted party was alleged as Catherine Marres. She testified that her name was Catherine Mars, pronounced in one syllable. It is sufficient to sustain an averment of the name charged, if the jury find that the name proved, although differently spelled, is substantially identical in pronunciation. 

Com. v. Butler, 1 Allen, 4 

Com. v. Darcey, 12 Allen, 539

The proper method of raising an objection for misnomer, and as a general rule, the only method, is a plea in abatement. 

Com. v. Fredericks, 119 Mass. 199

A misnomer of defendant in a complaint which is not apparent on the record cannot be availed of by a notion to dismiss, but only by a plea in abatement.

MISTRIAL 

Com. v. Cronin, 257 Mass. 535

A trying justice during a trial, after cautioning the attorney for the defendant to desist from practicing certain things, withdrew the case from the jury after the attorney had remarked to the court that his excluding certain evidence was against the Constitution and that his client was not getting a fair trial. This would not bar a second trial on the same indictment.

CRIMINAL CASES MONEY DEPOSITED GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 276, SEC. 69 

Way v. Day, 187 Mass. 477

A defendant cannot assign money deposited in court, to a third person. 

Tuck v. Manning, 150 Mass. 211

Money deposited in the hands of the clerk is not liable to a legal or equitable attachment. 

Reed v. Lowell Court, 172 Mass. 427

The defendant defaulted in the lower court and his bail money was forfeited. He subsequently came before the court and was held for the Superior Court. The court had the right to forfeit the money even if he afterwards came in. The clause in the bail bond that recites "until finally disposed of" does not apply in a case where the defendant defaults. If he had surrendered himself into court, it might have been different. MITTIMUS 

Martin v. Collins, 165 Mass. 256 

A prisoner was held on an incorrect mittimus. The jailor is not liable. He had no reason to disbelieve the mittimus was wrong, and there was no negligence on his part in failing to ascertain. 

Doherty v. Munson, 127 Mass. 495

A defendant is committed to the House of Correction under two mittimuses, one legal, one illegal. Held in custody under both, during the whole term of his imprisonment, he is lawfully imprisoned. 

When to file.

MOTION TO QUASH GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 278, SEC. 17 

Com. v. Markarian, 250 Mass. 211 

Com. v. Gurney, 261 Mass. 309

An objection to a complaint filed in the lower court based on a formal defect apparent on its face must be taken before a

CRIMINAL CASES 

357 judgment has been rendered by the lower court, and if it is not taken until after an appeal to the Superior Court and after a jury has been impaneled and the complaint read, it is too late. 

Com. v. Reid, 175 Mass. 325

It is too late to object in the Superior Court that a complaint filed originally in an inferior court is insufficient in form. 

Com. v. Drohan, 210 Mass. 445 

Com. v. Brigham, 108 Mass. 457

A motion to quash must be filed in the lower court. 

Com. v. Bailey, 199 Mass. 583

A motion to quash filed after a plea, no objection having been made, is deemed to be properly filed. 

Com. v. Lombardo, 271 Mass. 41

A motion to quash cannot be filed after a plea of not guilty on an indictment. It is then too late to attack the validity of the complaint. Chapter 278, Section 7, does not apply. At common law matters of abatement must be pleaded before a plea of not guilty. The same rules apply to a motion to quash. 

Com. v. Walsh, 255 Mass. 317

A plea of not guilty admits the validity of the indictment and while it stands precludes an attack thereon by a motion to quash. 

Com. v. Levanthal, 236 Mass. 516

Objections to an indictment relied on in a motion to quash could be raised only before a general plea of not guilty. 

Com. v. Kozlowsky, 238 Mass. 379

A motion to quash after a bill of particulars has been filed is too late, even if the indictment is faulty. 

Com. v. Dill, 159 Mass. 61

A motion in arrest of judgment for a cause existing before the verdict cannot be allowed unless it affects the jurisdiction of the court. 

Com. v. Washburn, 128 Mass. 421

A complaint which does not set forth the facts necessary to constitute an offense except by reference to a statute, the year of which is wrong, will not support a conviction, and the objection can be taken for the first time at the trial in the Superior Court on an appeal.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Donahoe, 130 Mass. 280

The objections to the complaint were in matters of form and not of substance, and not having been taken in the lower court, were not open in the Superior Court on appeal. 

Com. v. Hart, 123 Mass. 416

In the lower court a motion was filed attacking the form of the complaint, in the Superior Court the form of the warrant was attacked (not the complaint); it was too late in the Superior Court.

Form of. 

Com. v. Donovan, 170 Mass. 228

On the grounds that it does not allege sufficient facts to constitute a crime or set forth any offense known to the law, does not specifically assign objections. The motion must state specifically the objections. 

Com. v. Jenks, 138 Mass. 484

A motion to quash an indictment because the "same" is uncertain, indefinite, and insufficient does not assign specifically the objections relied on. 

When not allowed. 

Com. v. Peretz, 212 Mass. 253

An indictment which allows the defendant to understand the charge and prepare his defense shall not be quashed or considered defective. 

Complaint part good and part bad. 

Com. v. Pratt, 137 Mass. 98

If some of the complaint counts are good and some bad, the complaint will not be quashed for the defects in the bad ones. 

Com. v. Snell, 189 Mass. 12

A general motion to quash an indictment charged in three counts will not lie if any of the counts are good. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 105 Mass. 176

A motion filed with just the reason that certain precepts are "fatally defective" is not good. The objections must be specifically assigned.

CRIMINAL CASES 

359 Cause. 

Com. v. Galatta, 228 Mass. 308

A formal defect in an indictment cannot be taken advantage of by an arrest of judgment. 

Com. v. Kennedy, 131 Mass. 584

The statute providing that any objection for a formal defect apparent on its face shall be taken care of by demurrer or a motion to quash, does not apply to an indictment of a single count which duly charges an offense against a defendant, but contains a defective allegation of the aggravation of the offense. The offense was assault with intent to ravish but did not charge the aggravation. On a plea of guilty the defendant could only be sentenced for an assault. 

When filed. 

Com. v. Walton, 11 Allen, 238 

A complaint charging the keeping of a "tenement or shop" used for the illegal keeping and sale of liquors. The objection that this charge is bad, because made in the alternative, is merely formal, and must be taken before a judgment has been rendered in the original trial of the complaint. 

Com. v. Norton, 13 Allen, 550 

Com. v. Moscatiello, 257 Mass. 260

Formal objections to a complaint on which a conviction had been had in the lower court cannot be made for the first time after the case has been brought to the Superior Court on appeal. 

Com. v. Vincent, 108 Mass. 441

Formal objections to a complaint taken for the first time in the Superior Court on appeal from a conviction by a trial justice, and therefore invalid, cannot be reported for the determination to the Supreme Court, even after verdict, and with the consent of the defendant. 

Com. v. Doyle, 110 Mass. 103

A complaint to a trial justice charging an offense as committed after the date of the complaint will be quashed on objection taken after conviction and appeal notwithstanding the statute. 

Com. v. Harvey, 111 Mass. 420

Irregularities in the proceedings in the Municipal Court are no ground for dismissing the complaint on appeal, for the ap-

CRIMINAL CASES 

peal vacated the judgment below and gave the defendant his full rights in the court above. 

Com. v. Fay, 126 Mass. 235

Where a defect in the complaint is not formal, an arrest of judgment in the Superior Court may be entertained. 

Com. v. Fitchburg Rd., 126 Mass. 472

If a jury has once been impaneled in a criminal case, it is too late to move to quash the indictment for formal defects apparent on its face, although the motion was made before the impaneling of the jury for a new trial of the case, the former verdict having been set aside. 

Com. v. B. & M. R. R., 133 Mass. 383

Objections to an indictment not formal, defects apparent on the face, can be taken after the jury has been sworn. MOTIVE 

Com. v. Hudson, 97 Mass. 565

It is competent to prove that the defendant had a motive peculiar to himself, to commit the crime charged. 

Com. Richmond, 207 Mass. 240

The state is not obliged to show a motive for the commission of an alleged crime, but evidence of the existence of such a motive is competent and material.

MUNICIPAL OFFICER 

Com. v. Hunt, 216 Mass. 126

A deputy sealer of weights and measures is a municipal officer. 

Com. v. Tsaffaras, 250 Mass. 445

An examiner of the Registrar of Motor Vehicles is a municipal officer.

Form of complaint for bribery of a municipal officer.
