MURDER 

See also under Evidence. 

Com. v. York, 9 Met. 93

Malice discussed at length. On trial for killing, is proved to have been committed and nothing further is shown; the presumption of law is that it was malicious and an act of murder, and proof of the extenuating circumstances lies on the defendant. 

Com. v. McPike, 3 Cush. 181

Where a surgical operation is performed in a proper manner, which in the opinion of competent surgeons is necessary, and the person dies, the party inflicting the wound nevertheless is responsible for the consequences. 

Com. v. Drew, 4 Mass. 391

In a trespass against property if the trespasser is killed it is murder, but with an instrument not likely to kill if the trespasser dies, it is manslaughter. The deceased had not attempted to resist arrest and was killed before; hence it was murder. 

Com. v. Thompson, 6 Mass. 134

If one assuming the character of a physician through ignorance administers some medicine to a patient with an honest expectation of a cure, but it causes the death of the patient, he is not guilty of a felonious homicide. Murder must be done with malice, express or implied; if by ignorance he administers medicine, he cannot be convicted. 

Com. v. Bedrosian, 247 Mass. 573 

Com. v. Webster, 5 Cush. 295

Definition of first and second degree murder. Description of malice. If there was an intention to inflict injury on the deceased which was not justified on any lawful ground palliated by the existence of any mitigating circumstances, that intention was malicious within the meaning of the law. 

Com. v. Stewart, 255 Mass. 9

Statements made by the defendant charged with murder before the court charges the jury are not evidence, and no findings can be made by the jury on the strength of such statements, as every finding essential to a verdict must rest upon evidence presented in the usual way.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Pierce, 138 Mass. 165

A physician wrapping around a person clothes soaked with kerosene is guilty of manslaughter. The doctor's skill was one of gross negligence or foolhardy presumption. Need not plead in the indictment that the defendant knew that kerosene was of a dangerous tendency. 

Com. v. Zelenski, 287 Mass. 125

The rule that, in order to support a verdict of guilty at the trial of an indictment for homicide, there must be adequate proof of the corpus delicti without resort to admissions or confessions by the defendant, does not prevail in this state. 

Com. v. Webster, 5 Cush. 295

The term "homicide" in its largest sense is generic, embracing every mode by which the life of one man is taken by the act of another. 

Com. v. Campbell, 7 Allen, 541

A rioter cannot be held guilty of murder or manslaughter by reason of the accidental killing of an innocent person by those who are engaged in suppressing the riot. 

Com. v. Mink, 123 Mass. 422 

One who, in attempting to commit suicide, accidentally kills another who is trying to prevent it, is guilty of criminal homicide. 

Com. v. MacLoon, 101 Mass. 1

A conviction may be had for causing death by starvation or exposure. 

Time of death. 

Com. v. Parker, 2 Pick. 550

The mortal stroke, or the administering of poison, does not constitute the crime unless the sufferer dies within a year and a day. Cruelty. 

Com. v. Devlin, 126 Mass. 253

The cruelty manifested in causing death must be considered extreme, although it be possible to devise means of producing death which shall manifest a higher degree of criminality. It is enough if the means used were extreme as compared with ordinary means of producing death.

CRIMINAL CASES 

363 

Deliberation and premeditation. 

Com. v. Blackbourn, 7 Dane Abr. 210 

If one attack another with a deadly weapon without provocation, by assault and blows, intending deliberately to do him some great bodily harm, and death ensues, it is murder, for from the manner and weapon it shall be presumed he intended a felony; and from his doing the act without provocation the law supposes he did it designedly and from wickedness of heart, unless he shows the contrary. 

Com. v. Desmarteau, 82 Mass. 1

Murder committed with extreme atrocity or cruelty is murder in the first degree, although the atrocity or cruelty is not alleged or proved to have been premeditated. 

Deadly weapon. 

Com. v. Drew, 4 Mass. 391

A deadly weapon is one likely to produce death or great bodily injury. Insanity. 

Com. v. Rogers, 7 Met. 500

The defendant is not entitled to an acquittal on the ground of insanity, where, at the time of committing the offense, he had capacity to distinguish between right and wrong, understood the nature of his acts and their consequences, and had mental power enough to apply such knowledge to his own case. 

NAME 

See also under Misnomer. 

Parker v. Parker, 146 Mass. 320

In this state a middle name or initial is held to be a part of the name of a person and cannot be disregarded. 

NARCOTIC DRUGS GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 94, SECS. 197-217 

Com. v. Noble, 230 Mass. 83

It is a question for the jury whether a doctor prescribing drugs does it as a practitioner in an honest belief, or for gain.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Goldsmith, 176 Mass. 104 

Com. v. Marzynski, 149 Mass. 68 

Cigars and tobacco are not drugs. 

Burt v. Burt, 168 Mass. 204

The excessive use of narcotic drugs constituting drunkenness in a libel for divorce fully discussed. 

Com. v. Kane, 173 Mass. 477

The pleader need not set forth in the complaint any intent for the offense of being present when implements were found. 

NEGLIGENCE 

See under Automobile.

NOL PROSEQUI GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 12, SEC. 27 GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 277, SECS. 70A AND 70B 

Com. v. Uhrig, 167 Mass. 420

The district attorney can nol pros up to the time the jury is impaneled. 

Com. v. Dascalakis, 246 Mass. 12 

Com. v. Hart, 149 Mass. 7 

Com. v. Tuck, 20 Pick. 356

District attorney can nol pros after judgment. A nol pros without the consent of the defendant after the trial is commenced and before the verdict has the effect of an acquittal. The district attorney or the attorney general can nol pros in whole or in part. 

If done without the consent of the defendant, it is an acquittal. 

After the verdict the power to nol pros revives but after the final judgment the power ends. 

Com. v. Dunster, 145 Mass. 101

The district attorney can nol pros any count in a complaint charging many counts. He can nol pros in aggravated cases, the aggravation averred, and go to trial on the lesser crime. If two offenses improperly charged in one count, he can nol pros one of them.

CRIMINAL CASES 

365 

Com. v. Haskins, 128 Mass. 60

A nol pros cannot cure a defect in an illegal verdict returned by the jury. The defendant was charged with and convicted of larceny and receiving stolen goods. 

Com. v. Scott, 121 Mass. 33 

Com. v. Smith, 98 Mass. 10

Before the jury is impaneled or after a conviction, a nol pros may be entered without the consent of the defendant, but not during the trial. 

Atty. Gen. v. Tufts, 239 Mass. 458

The district attorney has the absolute power to nol pros on his own official responsibility without the approval or intervention of the court. 

Com. v. Hart, 149 Mass. 7

On a complaint in the lower court the defendant pleaded not guilty, and after the evidence was heard the judge nol prossed the case. Held a bar. The court had no right to nol pros. The record in the above case showed that the defendant was discharged. 

Com. v. McCluskey, 151 Mass. 488

The defendant was on trial in the Superior Court on an appeal from the lower court. The district attorney before the jury was impaneled nol prossed the case without the defendant's consent. The defendant later asked the court to be discharged from the complaint and from further answering to the charge. 

The court should have allowed the motion. Such discharge is not equivalent to an acquittal or to a general discharge from custody provided there is another charge against the defendant. 

Com. v. Cain, 102 Mass. 487

Three descriptions of the same crime in one indictment. The district attorney can elect on which count to go to trial and can nol pros the remainder even against the wish of the defendant. This is not an amendment to the indictment. 

Com. v. Beaman, 8 Gray, 497

A district attorney's signature to an indictment need not show for what district he is attorney. 

Com. v. Scott, 123 Mass. 222

In a criminal case the court may at the request of the district attorney appoint other counsel to assist him at the trial, if the

CRIMINAL CASES 

interests of public justice seem to require it, and such counsel acts without any assurance or expectation of compensation from any private person.

NUISANCES, STATUTORY AND COMMON LAW GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 139 

Single instances constituting. 

Com. v. Higgins, 16 Gray, 19

Proof that the defendant used for the illegal keeping and sale for any substantial period of time, if only for a day, will support an indictment for maintaining it for a period during a year. 

Com. v. Perley, 2 Cush. 559

A common seller of liquor may be convicted on evidence of making a sufficient number of unlawful sales on the same day. 

Com. v. Whalen, 16 Gray, 23

Three distinct sales are sufficient to convict for a nuisance. 

Bars, decanters, glasses, and other artifices used in the retailing and mixing of ardent spirits; in adjoining cellar were casks and kegs. 

Com. v. Gallagher, 1 Allen, 592 

Com. v. Oakes, 113 Mass. 8 

Chase v. Revere House, 232 Mass. 88

The defendant set up a tent on September 5th. On the next day from 9 A.M. to 11 A.M. he sold liquor, and the officers then took possession. The complaint alleged illegal keeping and sale. 

A nuisance can be maintained for the space of two hours. A disturbance of the public peace by the assembly of noisy and disorderly persons, the illegal sale of liquor, and other similar acts which tend to make disorder and injure public morals may have occurred in the course of a few hours as well as during a number of days, a week or a month. 

Com. v. Oakes, 113 Mass. 8

Outcries uttered in a public street, although they disturb but a single person, if they are accompanied by other circumstances necessary to constitute the offense are an indictable nuisance.

CRIMINAL CASES 

367 

Single instances not constituting. 

Com. v. Lambert, 12 Allen, 177

One single sale of liquor will not constitute a nuisance. 

Com. v. Hagan, 152 Mass. 565

A single sale of liquor in the absence of the defendant and not in the employ of the defendant will not warrant a conviction. Pleading. 

Com. v. Lapointe, 228 Mass. 266

A complaint for permitting a building to be used for the purposes of prostitution cannot be maintained on the evidence of letting. 

Com. v. Stahl, 7 Allen, 304

In an indictment for a nuisance the words "resorted to" must be averred. A person might use his premises for illegal gaming and not violate the law, but if he allows persons to resort there, he does. The pleader must aver that he kept, etc., for the purpose of illegal gaming and permitted persons to frequent there. 

Com. v. Donovan, 16 Gray, 18

Did keep and maintain a certain tenement, to wit, a room in a dwelling house used as a house of ill fame, gaming and liquor, whereby it is a nuisance; is a good complaint. 

Com. v. Howe, 13 Gray, 26

An indictment for a nuisance need not conclude "to the common nuisance of all citizens."

Com. v. Rumford Chem. Wks., 16 Gray, 231 

Form of complaint as to odors emanating from the manufacture of chemicals. 

Com. v. Buxton, 10 Gray, 9

No proof is required in an indictment for a nuisance or the allegation "to the great injury and common nuisance of all peaceable citizens." 

Com. v. Logan, 12 Gray, 136

The pleader need not allege where the building is located. 

Com. v. Skelly, 10 Gray, 464 "Keeping a certain tenement in Portland Street," etc., is sufficient without a more particular description.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Moore, 11 Cush. 600

Letting premises for prostitution is not a continuous offense, and a day certain must be averred. Also the name of the lessee and that the lease was received and accepted by the individual to whom it was made. 

Com. v. Brown, 13 Met. 365

The indictment averred smell, stenches, etc. The evidence must be confined to the above issuing from the building averred, and not from yards adjoining. 

Permitting a nuisance to exist. 

Com. v. Lapointe, 228 Mass. 266

Chapter 139, Section 20, of the General Laws charges three different crimes: (1) owns or has control of a building or tenement, knowingly lets it for the purposes of prostitution, assignation, or lewdness, (2) knowingly permits a building or tenement or any part thereof while under his control to be used for such purposes, (3) after due notice of such use omits to take all reasonable measures to eject therefrom the persons occupying the same as soon as it lawfully can be done shall be punished, etc. 

Com. v. Wentworth, 146 Mass. 36

The alleged control by a landlord over a liquor nuisance is negatived by proof that the tenant is in possession under a lease. 

Com. v. Bartley, 138 Mass. 181

The pleader must allege in the complaint the name of the person using the premises. 

Com. v. Bossidy, 112 Mass. 277

If the defendant let the whole building to the tenant and he used only a part of it, the complaint is good, but if the tenant only occupied a tenement in the building, it must be pleaded so; a building is a tenement but a tenement may be something different from a building. 

Com. v. Hayes, 167 Mass. 176

One who lets a part of the premises to another with the knowledge that liquor was being sold is guilty. This was not under a lease. 

Com. v. Burns, 167 Mass. 374

If the defendant had the sole control, he is guilty; if he carried it on jointly with another, he also would be guilty.

CRIMINAL CASES 

369 

Com. v. Bartley, 138 Mass. 181 "Knowingly permitting premises which were then and there under his control to be unlawfully used for the illegal sale and keeping of intoxicating liquors" is bad. It lacks certainty, not alleging the name of the person who sold or kept the liquor. 

Com. v. Lee, 148 Mass. 8

A building wholly occupied by the defendant, used by him in whole or in part, may be described as a building, or tenement, but a tenement is not necessarily an entire building, it may be a part of the building; and proof that the defendant occupied a part and used it illegally while other persons occupied the other parts will not support a charge of keeping and maintaining a building. 

Com. v. Churchill, 136 Mass. 148

The owner of a tenement house cannot be convicted if he made a written lease to another, even if he knew that the lessee intended to use it for the illegal sale of liquor and so used it. The servant of the lessee cannot be convicted if he made illegal sales in the presence and under the direct supervision of his employer. 

Com. v. Dunbar, 9 Gray, 298

Permitting premises under his control to be used for the illegal keeping of liquor. One Burns occupied a cellar of an inn conducted by the defendant, servants of the defendant carried liquor from the cellar to guests in the inn. It is a question for the jury as to knowledge. 

Kinds of nuisance. 

Victrola, radio. 

Stodder v. Rosen, 241 Mass. 245

The playing of a victrola or radio in a doorway of a store on a street is a nuisance. 

Bowling alley. 

Levin v. Goodwin, 191 Mass. 341

A bowling alley is not a nuisance and when licensed there is no redress in equity. 

Lunch cart. 

Com. v. Morrison, 197 Mass. 199

Maintaining a lunch cart in the street is a nuisance, even if licensed.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Hotel. 

Chace v. Revere House, 232 Mass. 88

The abatement under Chapter 139 of the General Laws as to houses of prostitution is constitutional. A single act of intercourse in a hotel may be sufficient when it is taken in conjunction with the reputation of the place and with other evidence. 

Dance Hall. 

Whitcomb v. Vigiant, 240 Mass. 359

The use of a hall in a city for public dances in accordance with a license granted by the mayor under Section 181 of Chapter 140 of the General Laws cannot be a nuisance, and could not subject the owner to an injunction or an indictment. 

Bell ringing. 

Davis v. Sawyer, 133 Mass. 289

The ringing of a huge bell at 6 A.M. for the purpose of arousing the keepers of lodging houses housing the mill workers is not a necessity, and evidence of customs elsewhere under similar circumstances is not admissible. 

Obstructing highway. 

Com. v. Franklin, 133 Mass. 569

In a complaint for obstructing a highway by building a sidewalk in front of his land, it is a question for the jury whether or not it is an unreasonable obstruction. 

Com. v. Gowen, 7 Mass. 378

An indictment lays for obstructing a highway, and the indictment need not allege the continuance of such with force and arms. 

Com. v. King, 13 Met. 115

Putting stones in a highway, even though the part is not used for travel, is a nuisance at common law. 

Com. v. Gowen, 7 Mass. 378

A nuisance lies on the town way. It need not be a public highway. 

Com. v. Low, 3 Pick. 408

Exclusive, uninterrupted use of a way for twenty years is evidence of a grant, but such way will be a private way and a nuisance on it is indictable.

CRIMINAL CASES 

371 

Com. v. Blaisdell, 107 Mass. 234

Steps projecting into a highway from a house so as to obstruct it are a nuisance at common law. 

Com. v. Surridge, 265 Mass. 425

Speaking from a box in the street, thereby gathering crowds, is not an exercise of the easement of travel and is a nuisance. A city council has no right to grant a permit to make a speech in the highway, and even if there is no inconvenience or delay to public travel, it is indictable as a common law nuisance. 

Com. v. Noxon, 121 Mass. 42

The owner of property has a reasonable time to remove property taken by eminent domain, and if he neglects to do so he forfeits the property so abandoned; but he does not thereby become liable and indictable for a criminal act or an admission of duty. Odors. 

Com. v. Kidder, 107 Mass. 188

The emission of odors and vapors from a refinery and the sale of petroleum are a common law nuisance. 

Com. v. Brown, 13 Met. 365

The indictment alleged a nuisance of smells and stenches, and the evidence must be confined to the above issue of the same coming from the building alleged, and not from the yard adjoining. Naphtha. 

Com. v. Packard, 185 Mass. 64

The storing of naphtha. A license to store the same should have been admitted in evidence. Health. 

Com. v. Collins, 257 Mass. 580

A complaint under Section 123 of Chapter 111 of the General Laws to remove a nuisance within 24 hours is not unreasonable; the notice need not be signed by the Commissioner, a notice by the secretary is sufficient. Under Section 75 of Chapter 233 copies of records duly attested are admissible. No notice necessary before the legal notice is sent. 

Com. v. Sweeney, 131 Mass. 579

Collecting certain filth, etc., and allowing the same to remain near to a dwelling house of several persons and near to a high-

CRIMINAL CASES 

way, to the great damage and common nuisance of all citizens in this commonwealth. 

City of Salem v. Ry. Co., 98 Mass. 431 

An order of a board of health for the removal of a nuisance is valid without previous notice to the parties interested and opportunity for them to appear and be heard. The board need not prescribe a mode for the removal, but if it does, the owner or occupant need not be bound by that mode but may remove it in any suitable manner. 

Watuppa Reservoir v. Mackenzie, 132 Mass. 71 

An order of a board of health directing the owner of land to remove a nuisance in a specific manner is void. The order of the board directing the owner to remove a nuisance is void, if passed without a previous notice and hearing. 

Com. v. Young, 135 Mass. 526 

The keeping of one hundred and fifty swine and the carting of offal to feed them from a neighboring city is an "employment.' 

A prosecution for the removal of them must be brought under the regulation of "an assignment by a board of health for places for the exercising of any trade or employment which is hurtful to inhabitants or dangerous to public health.” 

Com. v. Alden, 143 Mass. 113

An order to remove a notice may be served by a constable even though he is a member of the board and signs the notice. An order to remove a nuisance and remove certain hogs outside the city limits is not void by the direction as to the manner in which the hogs should be removed, as the order is to abate the nuisance. 

A complaint may be made by the agent of the board. An omission in the complaint that the complainant is an agent of the board of health, he being in fact such agent, is at most a formal defect which can be availed only by a motion to quash. 

Stone v. Heath, 179 Mass. 385

The Superior Court will not grant an injunction to prevent a local board of health from abating a nuisance. The board has jurisdiction under Section 122 of Chapter 111 over the purity of water supply as well as other causes of sickness. 

Com. v. Drew, 208 Mass. 493

Section 122 of Chapter 111 does not give the board the right to regulate the selling or how milk shall be kept. In the phrase

CRIMINAL CASES 

373 "nuisances, source of filth and causes of sickness," the phrase 'causes of sickness" is to be interpreted by the doctrine of noscitur a sociis. It is a little broader term than the two terms that precede it, but it is of the same general character. 

Wheeler v. Boston, 233 Mass. 275

Transporting garbage which was collected from hotels and restaurants by an individual is not within the law. The City of 

Boston granted a permit to a certain corporation to collect all the garbage in the city. The court will not allow a writ of mandamus to compel the city to grant a permit to an outside individual. The city possesses the right under its police power to confine this collecting to a single individual or a corporation. Gaming. 

Com. v. Charley Joe, 193 Mass. 383

The commonwealth need not show that the whole of the premises were used, if one room is used it is sufficient; nor that gaming was the principal purpose, it is sufficient if it is one of the purposes. The commonwealth need not show that it was open to the public. It is sufficient to show that any number of persons were in the habit of resorting to the place for illegal gaming. If the place was resorted to on one occasion only, the defendant is not liable; if commonly used the nuisance would be made out. 

Garvey v. McNulty, 270 Mass. 260

Every building place or tenement resorted to for illegal gaming is a nuisance. 

Com. v. Baker, 155 Mass. 287

Form of complaint for gaming nuisance. The state need not show the ownership by the defendant; that during some period alleged he had the sole charge, control, and management while the place was resorted to, is sufficient. 

Com. v. Warren, 161 Mass. 281 

Com. v. Ward, 281 Mass. 119

The word "place" as used in being found present refers to the house or building and is not confined to the room where such persons were found when arrested. A thick barricaded door, a peep hole through which the keeper looked, a partition where the implements were concealed.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Taylor, 14 Gray, 26

The indictment charged the defendant with keeping and maintaining a liquor, gaming, and house of ill fame nuisance in the one indictment. All gaming is unlawful, and it is gaming to play any game of hazard for money or other article of value. A game of hazard to determine who shall pay for the drinks is strictly playing for money. It is a question for the jury to determine whether persons resorted to the place for the purpose of gaming. 

In general, it is a fair conclusion to hold that persons intend to do that which they habitually do, and if one of the purposes of persons resorting to the defendant's house was gaming, a case was made out. 

Com. v. Donovan, 16 Gray, 18 

Form of complaint for gaming nuisance. 

Com. v. Stahl, 7 Allen, 303

An owner or keeper of a house may engage in an unlawful sport or game therein. He may use the premises which he owns or occupies without violating the statute. So long as he does not permit persons to resort therein for illegal gaming he does not maintain a common nuisance. 

House of ill fame. See also under separate title. 

Com. v. Lambert, 12 Allen, 177

Suffering a single act of illicit intercourse in a charge of keeping a house of ill fame or lascivious behavior or exposure of one's person is not keeping a nuisance. Lewdness in the above sense includes private illicit intercourse as well as public indecency. 

Liquor. See under separate title. 

Club. See also under Alcoholic beverages. 

Com. v. Baker, 152, Mass. 337

In a club not licensed, liquors are dispensed to members on an order for liquor belonging to a member; is indictable as a nuisance. 

Com. v. Fleckner, 167 Mass. 13

The state need not show that a club is used merely for the selling and the keeping of liquor. 

Com. v. Ryan, 152 Mass. 283

Persons had liquor in their pocket. Fixtures resembling a bar room, many men here found drinking, men drinking out of bot-

CRIMINAL CASES 

375 tles not bearing the owner's name. Merely a subterfuge, and an indictment will prevail. 

Com. v. Woelz, 219 Mass. 37

A steward sold liquors to members of an incorporated club; the club had no license, the steward is guilty. It is no defense that he is an employee. 

Com. v. Pomphret, 137 Mass. 564

Members pay an admission fee on joining, no outsiders own any liquor. The club employs a steward who dispenses the liquor to members, who pay for it with checks representing sums which are purchased from the steward. The money received is used by the club for purchasing liquor. Not guilty of exposing and keeping. 

Com. v. Smith, 102 Mass. 144

The members of a club jointly contributed money to purchase of liquor by the steward. The steward issued checks to the members, who in return purchased the liquor, using the checks. If the jury finds that the liquor belonged to the members and that the steward merely kept the liquors, he is not guilty; if they find that this is an evasion, then he is guilty. 

Whole building. 

Com. v. Shattuck, 14 Gray, 23

An indictment for keeping a building, etc., is supported by proof that the defendant occupied the whole building and used a part of it illegally. 

Apartment in a building. 

Com. v. McCaughey, 9 Gray, 296

Keeping an apartment in a building, which apartment is held separately from the rest of the building, is not keeping and maintaining a building, but is the keeping and maintaining of a tenement within the meaning of the statute. 

Abatement of a.

Tortorello v. Traiser Co., 284 Mass. 497 

While a noise may constitute an actionable nuisance, it must be such a noise as affects injuriously the health or comfort of ordinary people in the vicinity to an unreasonable extent.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Private citizen abating a. 

Brown v. Perkins, 12 Gray, 89

A private citizen cannot abate a nuisance unless it obstructs his own right.

Aiding and assisting in a. 

Com. v. Gannett, 1 Allen, 7

One who aided and assisted others in keeping a house of ill fame is guilty of keeping the same. 

Com. v. Mann, 4 Gray, 213

An agent of the owner who paid workmen, was around overseeing, can be convicted of aiding and promoting and encouraging acts alleged in the indictment. Eviction.

Sullivan v. Vorenburg, 241 Mass. 319 

The lessor can terminate a lease on a conviction for using the premises for illegal gaming, as one of the covenants in the lease was that the same was to be used for business purposes only. Awning. 

Anderson v. Kopelman, 279 Mass. 140

An awning extending over but less than six feet one inch above the sidewalk surface in violation of an ordinance is a nuisance.

OATH 

Com. v. Burrough, 162 Mass. 513

The defendant has no right without being sworn as a witness to make a statement to the jury. 

Com. v. Lynes, 142 Mass. 577

A child 13 years of age. It is within the discretion of the court after questioning the child as to whether she shall be sworn as a witness.

OLEOMARGARINE GEN LAWS, CHAP. 94, SECS. 49-63 

Com. v. Huntley, 156 Mass. 236

Made in imitation of butter (yellow), is not a violation of interstate commerce.

CRIMINAL CASES 

377 

Com. v. Kelly, 163 Mass. 169

The coloring of oleo artificially is constitutional. 

Com. v. Byrnes, 158 Mass. 172

Colored in imitation of yellow butter and kept in a refrigerator, is not exposing for sale. Illegal exposing for sale is uniformly joined with that of illegal keeping. 

Com. v. Russell, 162 Mass. 520 

Cannot expose for sale oleo colored to look like butter, and it is immaterial whether the particular customer was advised of its character or not. 

Com. v. Crane, 158 Mass. 218

The defendant was selling oleo from a wagon which was a covered one, a place card was inside the cover which could be seen from the front or rear. Guilty. 

Com. v. Stewart, 159 Mass. 113

The proprietor of a restaurant can be convicted of serving oleo notwithstanding a sign was posted on the walls and on the bill of fare. No oral notice was given. 

Com. v. Bean, 148 Mass. 172

Oleo was exposed for sale in the original package in a tub. The top of the cover as well as the side and the bottom, had been marked in accordance with law. The cover had been removed, disclosing the surface of the oleo without any mark. The terms of the statute had been complied with. 

Com. v. Gray, 150 Mass. 327

A complaint charging the defendant with selling imitation butter at retail without a descriptive wrapper need not allege the sale was made actually by the defendant's agent. 

Com. v. Crane, 162 Mass. 506

The statute was not intended to draw fine distinctions between different kinds of oleo, all of which resemble butter; but it requires that everyone who delivers oleo of whatever sort, from a vehicle upon a public street, shall carry along with him upon his vehicle a public notice that he is licensed to sell oleo. 

Com. v. Russell, 162 Mass. 520

An act to prevent deception in the manufacture and sale of imitation butter forbids the exposing for sale of oleo colored to look like butter, and it is immaterial whether the particular purchaser was advised of its real character or not.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Mills, 157 Mass. 405

The defendant was charged with possession of oleo in a tub, the same not properly marked. The evidence showed the possession of the oleo in the tub, but this fact did not warrant a conviction that it was intended for sale. 

Com. v. McDonnell, 157 Mass. 407 

Same as Com. v. Mills, 157 Mass. 405.

OPTOMETRY GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 112, SECS. 66-73 

Com. v. Houtenbrink, 235 Mass. 320

One who examines and tries instruments on the eyes and puts a designation after his name is practicing optometry without being licensed. 

Com. v. S. S. Kresge Co., 267 Mass. 145

A store carried eyeglasses on display and a card bearing different sizes of printing matter. The customers were advised to read the printed matter and when they found what they deemed the proper glasses, the clerk gave them a pair from the counter corresponding to the number they selected. The corporation was guilty of practicing optometry without a license. 

OBSCENITY GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 272, SEC. 28 AMENDED ACTS 1934, CHAP. 231 

Search warrant for obscene pictures. 

Com. v. Parker, 59 Mass. 369 When a party is convicted upon an indictment, the court can order the pictures, etc., destroyed or impounded, even though they were seized on a defective warrant unlawfully issued and served. Pleading. 

Com. v. Holmes, 17 Mass. 336

In an indictment for publishing an obscene book it is sufficient to give a general description thereof, and aver the evil tendency, without copying the book or minutely describing the print.

CRIMINAL CASES 

379 

Com. v. Allison, 227 Mass. 57

A description of an obscene pamphlet without enumerating any description is good; a bill of particulars is the remedy. 

Com. v. McCance, 164 Mass. 162

As to the descriptions of an obscene book and parts of a book. 

Com. v. Dejardin, 126 Mass. 46

An indictment alleged that the defendant printed and published pictures of naked girls. This is not sustained by proof that he published obscene pictures of girls naked only above the waist. 

Com. v. Wright, 139 Mass. 382

In setting out the complaint of the obscene picture or thing, there must be at least some general description so as to identify the paper. The pleader need not set it out in toto but may allege that it is too obscene to be more fully described in the complaint.

Evidence. 

Com. v. DeLacey, 271 Mass. 327

An investigator of the Watch and Ward procured the defendant to get a book, the same not being in stock, and this was done, and sold to the investigator. This constituted a sale; the defendant was not merely the agent of the investigator. The fact that the defendant had made previous sales of the same book made inapplicable the doctrine that because he was entrapped into selling this book by false pretenses and persuasion, he could not be found to have intended to commit it. 

Com. v. Friede, 271 Mass. 318

The court can exclude the book in question and only admit passages relied upon for the obscenity. 

Description of obscenity — manifestly intending, etc. 

Com. v. Buckley, 200 Mass. 346 "Obscene" means offensive to morality, to chastity; indecent, nasty; "impure" explains itself. Language is offensive, impure, and indecent when it manifestly tends to incite in the minds of people susceptible to such influences, obscene thoughts, impure thoughts, indecent thoughts. It is not sufficient under the clause of the statute (manifestly intending to corrupt the morals of

CRIMINAL CASES 

youth) that the language be such as might tend to corrupt the thoughts of the young; it must obviously and incontrovertibly have that tendency. 

Com. v. Allison, 227 Mass. 57

The distribution of pamphlets which would promote wantonness is an offense, though they were statements of scientific facts. 

The defendant distributed pamphlets describing birth control. 

OTHER THINGS 

Com. v. Dejardin, 126 Mass. 46

The general words "or other things" must be construed to mean "or other things of a like kind." The maxim "noscitur a sociis" applies-one is known by the company he keeps. 

PADLOCK LAW GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 139, SEC. 16A 

Reale v. Justices of Sup. Ct., 265 Mass. 135

The padlock law is constitutional. It provides for civil proceedings to abate a nuisance and is no violation of the provisions of the federal law or state constitution prohibiting penal statutes which are ex post facto in their operation. 

Chase v. Revere House, 232 Mass. 88

The abatement under Chapter 139 as to houses of prostitution is constitutional. A single act of prostitution, to wit, intercourse in a hotel, may be sufficient when it is taken in conjunction with the reputation of the place and with other evidence.

The parading of people without a permit is unlawful. May

PARK COMMISSIONERS 

Com. v. Crowninshield, 187 Mass. 221

The park commissioners have the right to take over Commonwealth Avenue from Arlington Street to Beacon Street. 

Statutes as to the automobile laws do not abrogate the rules of the park commissioners. 

Com. v. Butler, 204 Mass. 11

A public way under the jurisdiction of the park commissioners is still a public way and may be pleaded as such; the commissioners only exercise jurisdiction over it. 

Com. v. Newhall, 205 Mass. 344 

Com. v. Abrahams, 156 Mass. 57

A statute which repeals all existing park regulations is valid.

The prohibiting of signs near a parkway is not a reasonable regulation, being contrary to the constitution. It is taking property for a public use and not providing compensation.

PASSENGER 

Com. v. B. & M. Ry., 129 Mass. 500

One who has bought a ticket or is otherwise entitled to transportation on a particular train of cars of a railroad corporation is ordinarily a passenger, from the time when he reasonably and properly starts from the ticket office or waiting room in the station to take his seat in the car of the train until he has reached the station to which he is entitled to be carried, and has had an opportunity by safe and convenient means to leave the train and roadway of the corporation at that station. 

PAWNBROKER GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 140, SECS. 70-85 

Com. v. Schwartz, 197 Mass. 107

A pawnbroker may be licensed to buy and sell secondhand good and articles, . making a loan on a coat, he was com-

CRIMINAL CASES 

plained of for conducting a pawnbroker's business without a license. One act may be sufficient. The occupation and not the business is complained of. 

Com. v. Danziger, 176 Mass. 290

Form of complaint for conducting a pawnbroker's business without a license. The act is constitutional. 

Round v. Police Com'r., 197 Mass. 218

The records of the transactions of a pawnbroker required by law to be furnished once a week to the licensing board or to any person designated by the board, is not a public record to which all persons may have access and of which they are entitled to be furnished with copies. It is not within the definition of a public record under the law which includes, among other things, any paper which any officer or employee of the commonwealth or of a county, city, or town has received or is required to receive for filing.

PEACEFUL PERSUASION 

Godin v. Niebuhr, 236 Mass. 350

General Laws, Chapter 149, Section 24, does not purport to excuse unlawful conduct such as the publication of libelous matter coercive in its nature. The purpose may be lawful, but the means employed may be unlawful. 

Rice Barton v. Willard, 242 Mass. 566

The provisions of the statute are not applicable to a strike originally begun for a lawful purpose but unlawfully conducted. Pleading.

PERJURY GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 268 

Com. v. Johnson, 175 Mass. 152

The indictment need not set forth the fact that at the time of asking the witness questions a copy of the conviction of the witness was in the hands of the inquirer. 

Com. v. Bouvier, 164 Mass. 398

The pleader need not allege that the court was held for criminal business if the allegation was before a district court.

CRIMINAL CASES 

383 

Com. v. Aronson, 250 Mass. 521 

Com. v. Carel, 105 Mass. 582

One committed a surety offering a false title to property. Form of complaint. 

Com. v. Devine, 155 Mass. 224

Perjury and subornation of perjury can be joined in the same complaint. 

Com. v. Stone, 152 Mass. 498

In an indictment for subornation it is not necessary to allege the residence of the person whose testimony is suborned, but such residence if alleged is descriptive of the offense and must be proved as alleged. 

Com. v. McCarthy, 152 Mass. 577

The substantive matter may be shown to be material by a direct averment or by allegations from which the materiality appears. 

Com. v. Knight, 12 Mass. 274

The pleader need not allege that the witnesses were summoned. It is held sufficient to allege that the perjury was committed in an issue duly joined without an express allegation that the cause of action was within the jurisdiction of the court. 

The pleader must allege that facts representing were in answer to a material fact. 

Com. v. Hughes, 5 Allen, 499

It is not necessary to set forth the authority of the person before whom the offense was committed. 

Com. v. Byron, 14 Gray, 31

The indictment must allege that the question on which the perjury is based is a material one. 

Com. v. Hatfield, 107 Mass. 227

It is not necessary to set forth the authority of the court or persons before whom the perjury was committed. The offense consists in the false statements of material facts under oath, knowing them to be false. It is not necessary to prove falsity of the statements in all of the details. 

Com. v. Terry, 114 Mass. 263 

Form of complaint for falsely answering interrogatories.

CRIMINAL CASES Evidence. 

Com. v. Sargent, 129 Mass. 115

To convict of perjury, the state must introduce the testimony of two witnesses showing the falsity of the material statement, or of one witness and facts equivalent to the evidence of a second witness. If a person merely makes inadvertently an error in judgment, or carelessness in valuing property, or carelessly and without any intent to mislead the state, the defendant is not guilty. 

Com. v. Pollard, 12 Met. 225

The testimony of a single witness is sufficient to prove that the defendant swore as averred in the complaint. The question may be to a point which is only circumstantially material to the question in dispute. Definition of the crime of perjury. 

Com. v. Douglass, 5 Met. 241

Subornation may be proved by the testimony of one witness. 

To constitute subornation, the party charged must procure the commission by inciting, instigating, or persuading the witness to commit the crime. 

Com. v. Smith, 11 Allen, 243

One may be convicted in this state of subornation of perjury who procures the commission of the perjury in this state through the agency of another party (guilty) without the limits of this state. 

Com. v. Brady, 5 Gray, 78

False swearing to a fact to the best of the opinion of the witness, which the witness thought without any reasonable cause, believes the same to be true, is not perjury. 

Com. v. Carel, 105 Mass. 582

On an indictment charging giving a false affidavit, the lack of a signature is immaterial. The signature is not part of the declaration but merely authenticates it. 

Com. v. Grant, 116 Mass. 17 

To swear corruptly and falsely as to any material consequence which has a legitimate tendency to prove or disprove the fact in issue is perjury.

CRIMINAL CASES 

385 

Com. v. Johnson, 175 Mass. 152

A defendant on trial commits perjury when he falsely swears that he was never before arrested, convicted, or sentenced for a crime.

Com. v. Dunham Thatchers Crim. Cases, 519 

A cashier who swears that the return made by him "is according to his best knowledge and belief true," knowing at the time that it is false, and taking the oath deliberately, is guilty. 

Com. v. Wright, 166 Mass. 174

Where an indictment avers that the defendant was called and sworn as a witness, it necessarily implies that he was "lawfully required to depose the truth in a proceeding in a court of justice." 

Oath-basis for perjury-authority. 

Com. v. Kimball, 108 Mass. 473

Under the rule of the Superior Court a person is compelled to take an oath to certain writs; not by law, however. An oath administered to a party in such a proceeding for the purpose of establishing the truth of the facts stated is an oath lawfully administered in the regular course of a judicial proceeding. 

Avery v. Ward, 150 Mass. 160

Swearing to an insurance policy is an oath required by law. 

Procedure when committed before the court. 

Blankenburg v. Com., 272 Mass. 25

The defendant committed perjury in the probate court and was immediately ordered into custody. She gave a bond for her appearance subsequently for a hearing. No process is necessary to give the court jurisdiction of the offender and he may be taken into custody at once. To punish as a criminal contempt committed in the presence of the court, there must be added to the essential elements of perjury the further element of obstructing the court in the performance of its duty. 

PERMISSION 

Com. v. Kennedy, 160 Mass. 312

A statute requiring that no one shall board a ship without permission, this is not met by going on board and obtaining permission afterwards.

CRIMINAL CASES PHARMACISTS AND PHARMACY GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 112, SECS. 24-36 

Munkley v. Hoyt, 179 Mass. 108

The defendant pleaded guilty to a liquor law violation. The complaint was placed on file by the court. This is such a conviction under the law as enables the Board of Pharmacy to suspend the license.

PILOT GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 103, SEC. 35 

Com. v. Kemp, 254 Mass. 190

Under General Laws, Chapter 103, Sections 28 and 35, the law relates to between states and must be interpreted by decisions of the United States.

PLACED ON FILE GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 218, SEC. 38 

Marks v. Wentworth, 199 Mass. 44

A complaint cannot be placed on file without the consent of the defendant. After probation a complaint may be placed on file, but dismissing the complaint is the better way. 

Dowdicans Bail Case, 115 Mass. 133

Placing on file is not equivalent to a final judgment or a nol pros or a discontinuance. The court may take it from the files at any time. 

Com. v. Maloney, 145 Mass. 205

If the magistrate has the authority to order a party into custody, he cannot continue the case indefinitely and hold the defendant to appear at any time to be afterwards named. 

Com. v. Brown, 167 Mass. 144

The defendant was found guilty, the verdict was set aside on motion of the defendant, and the complaint was placed on file. On being indicted on the same facts later, he cannot plead former jeopardy.

CRIMINAL CASES 

387 

Munkley v. Hoyt, 179 Mass. 108

The defendant, a pharmacist, was found guilty of a liquor violation. This case was then placed on file. This is a conviction in so far as to permit the Board of Pharmacy to suspend the license. 

Bacon v. Waters, 2 Allen, 400

By placing an indictment on file the prosecution is not ended. 

The defendant is liable at any time to be called to answer to the charge.

PLEA 

Plea of not guilty admits all inaccuracies. 

Com. v. Levanthal, 236 Mass. 516 

Com. v. Lannan, 13 Allen, 563

After a plea of not guilty the defendant cannot file another plea without leave of the court, so after a plea of not guilty in the lower court and trial thereon the defendant cannot file another plea in the Superior Court without leave of the court. After filing an invalid plea in bar, he may properly be required to plead further, and if he refuses, a plea of not guilty may be entered. 

Com. v. Moscatiello, 257 Mass. 260

Matters in abatement are waived by going to trial in a district court without objection to the complaint. 

Plea of not guilty. 

Com. v. Levanthal, 236 Mass. 516

The plea of not guilty to an indictment is an admission of its validity and a waiver of all matters of abatement, and no motion can be filed after this plea without leave of the court. 

Lebowich v. Com., 235 Mass. 357

The soundness of an indictment is admitted by a general plea. 

Com. v. Wakelin, 230 Mass. 567

Whether a plea of guilty or not guilty, entered either by the defendant or his counsel, may be retracted and other pleadings entered is wholly a matter for the court to determine. The defendant may plead and reserve his rights to substitute other pleadings in a nature preliminary.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Butler, 1 Allen, 4

It is too late after a plea of not guilty to object to the wrong or defective description of the defendant in the complaint. 

Plea of nolo contendere. 

Com. v. Tilton, 8 Met. 232

This plea, like a demurrer, admits for the purpose of the case all the facts which are well stated, but it is not to be used as an admission elsewhere. 

Com. v. Horton, 9 Pick. 206

A plea of nolo contendere has the same effect as a plea of guilty, so far as regards the proceedings on the indictment and a defendant who is sentenced on such a plea is convicted of the offense for which he is indicted. But a plea with a protestation of the defendant's innocence will not preclude him in a civil action from disputing the facts charged in the indictment. 

Com. v. Adams, 6 Gray, 359

On a plea of nolo the plea can only be made with the consent of the state, and if a defendant in the lower court pleads nolo and that consent is not made a part of the record on appeal, the defendant can plead anew. 

Olszewski v. Goldberg, 223 Mass. 27

A plea of nolo is equivalent to a plea of guilty. It is in fact a confession. A conviction following a plea of nolo cannot be used in another proceeding to affect the credibility of a witness. 

White v. Creamer, 175 Mass. 567

A sentence imposed after a plea of nolo amounts to a conviction in the case in which it is entered. A record showing a conviction on such a plea is not admissible in another proceeding to show that the defendant was guilty. Such a plea is merely an implied confession, not an express one. 

Com. v. Marino, 254 Mass. 533

The plea of nolo admits the guilt, and on an appeal does not entitle the defendant to a trial. As to the withdrawing of the plea in the Superior Court, it is within the discretion of the court. 

Com. v. Ingersoll, 145 Mass. 381

A plea of nolo, when accepted by the lower court, is in its affect upon the case equivalent to a plea of guilty. If the plea is

CRIMINAL CASES 

389 accepted, it is not necessary for the court to adjudge the party guilty, for it is an implied confession of guilt and the court proceeds to sentence. An implied confession of guilt only, and cannot be used in a civil suit for the same act as an admission. 

Plea in abatement. 

Turns v. Com., 6 Met. at 235

If on arraignment the defendant does not plea in abatement, he admits himself rightly designated. 

Com. v. Gedzium, 259 Mass. 453

A mistake or error in the name of the defendant in an indictment is not necessarily fatal to its validity. It is grounds for a motion to dismiss, but only for a plea in abatement. If the defendant on his arraignment does not plea in abatement, he admits himself rightly designated. The issue is not in the name but whether the person who has pleaded in chief is guilty of the crime charged. 

Com. v. Fredericks, 119 Mass. 199

An alleged misnomer of the defendant not being apparent on the record cannot be availed of by a motion to dismiss but only by a plea in abatement. 

Com. v. Harris, 231 Mass. 584

A plea in abatement must be sustained if witnesses other than witness testifying were in the grand jury room, although it appears that they were there for the purpose of testifying, and that the defendant was not injured by their presence. 

Com. v. Farrell, 105 Mass. 189

After a jury had been impaneled, the defendant on his plea of not guilty was allowed to withdraw that plea and file a plea in abatement for misnomer, which was sustained and the indictment quashed. This was not former jeopardy to another indictment for the same offense. 

Com. v. Cody, 165 Mass. 133

The pendency of an indictment is no grounds for a plea in abatement to another indictment in the same court for the same offense. 

Com. v. Carr, 114 Mass. 280

If one accused of a misdemeanor pleads misnomer on a plea in abatement and the issue in fact is joined thereon, and found

CRIMINAL CASES 

against him by the jury, he cannot as a matter of right plead over. 

If his plea in abatement is demurred to, and overruled as a matter of law, it is otherwise. 

Com. v. Darcy, 12 Allen, 539

A plea of misnomer can be taken advantage of only by a plea in abatement, and a plea of guilty in a criminal complaint is a waiver of any objection of a misnomer. 

Misnomer. See under Plea in abatement, above. Retraction. 

Com. v. Ingersoll, 145 Mass. 381 

Com. v. Wakelin, 230 Mass. 567

A defendant cannot retract a plea of guilty and plead not guilty except by leave of the court. 

Com. v. Soderquest, 183 Mass. 199

After a defendant has been sentenced in the Superior Court on a plea of guilty, the court has no authority to allow the defendant to retract this plea and plead not guilty. 

Com. v. Mahoney, 115 Mass. 151 

Com. v. Crapo, 212 Mass. 209 

Com. v. Winton, 108 Mass. 485

A plea of guilty in the lower court is a bar to a trial in the Superior Court. The court may permit the defendant to withdraw and plead not guilty, or upon a motion, if defects not apparent on the face are shown. 

Plea in bar. 

Com. v. Wermouth, 174 Mass. 74

If a defendant pleads in bar, the burden of proof is on him to show it. 

Com. v. Merrill, 8 Allen, 545 

Com. v. Bakemen, 105 Mass. 53

A general plea of not guilty ought not to be joined in a plea in bar. He cannot be put on trial against his objections upon the plea of not guilty until the first is decided against him. Autrefois acquit and autrefois convict must be pleaded specially and are not admissible under a plea of not guilty.

CRIMINAL CASES 

391 Court may enter a plea. 

Ellenwood v. Com., 10 Met. 222 

Com. v. Moore, 9 Mass. 402

If the defendant was asked to plead and he remained mute and after a due examination and a full trial he was adjudged guilty, no remedy lies. 

Com. v. McKenna, 125 Mass. 397

If the defendant remains mute, a plea of not guilty may be entered; a statement on appeal by the clerk that he pleaded not guilty is only an informality and is waived by going to trial without objecting. 

Com. v. Quirk, 155 Mass. 296

If a defendant refuses to plead, the court may direct that a plea of not guilty be entered. 

First degree. 

Com. v. Battis, 1 Mass. 95

The court will not direct an immediate plea of guilty be accepted in a capital case but will give the defendant a reasonable time to consider the same. 

Green v. Com., 12 Allen at 173

When a defendant pleads guilty, he confesses and convicts himself of all that is duly charged against him. A plea of not guilty puts in issue every fact. One may plead guilty to a first degree indictment.

Change of plea in the Superior Court. 

Com. v. Hagerman, 10 Allen, 401

The defendant cannot retract his plea of guilty in the Superior Court on an appeal only by permission of the court; and if the motion is refused and a jury impaneled, they may return a verdict of guilty without the introduction of any evidence. 

Com. v. Blake, 12 Allen, 188

In the Superior Court on appeal the defendant has no right to withdraw his plea of not guilty upon which the case was tried in the lower court and plead anew, except by leave of the court.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Pleading to a part of the complaint. 

O'Connell v. Com., 7 Met. 460

The indictment charged the defendant with receiving stolen goods of various articles, and specifically designated each and the value thereof. The defendant pleads that he is guilty of receiving $50 worth of the alleged property. Bad. No valid judgment can be rendered on such a plea. 

PLEADING 

See also under the separate headings of crimes.

Steal-definition. 

Com. v. Koslowsky, 238 Mass. 379

The word "steal" has acquired a meaning that includes the criminal taking of the personal property of another with the intent to deprive the owner permanently of the use of it. 

Com. v. Pratt, 132 Mass. 246 

A complaint must allege "did steal, take, and carry away"; the omission of the word "away" is fatal. 

Enacting clause. 

Com. v. Jennings, 121 Mass. 47 

Com. v. Hart, 11 Cush. 130

It is a general rule in pleading that when an exception or proviso is embodied in the same clause which defines the crime or is commonly called the enacting clause, it must be negatived in the indictment; but when it is found only in a subsequent distinct clause of the same or another statute, it need not be negatived. 

Com. v. Lee, 247 Mass. 107

When an exception or proviso is embodied in the provisions of a municipal ordinance which defines the offense, it must be negatived in the complaint. The necessity of such negation is not avoided by the provisions of Section 33 of Chapter 277 of the General Laws. 

Com. v. Maxwell, 2 Pick. 139

Where the penalty provided for a fine for each person illegally entertained at an inn on the Lord's day, the complaint must allege specifically how many.

CRIMINAL CASES 

393 

Dates in a complaint. 

Com. v. Sego, 125 Mass. 210

It is not necessary to prove the time as alleged in an indictment for larceny. 

Benson v. Com., 158 Mass. 164

In an indictment for the offense of poisoning, the date is purely technical and need not be proved as laid. 

Com. v. McMahon, 133 Mass. 394

A complaint in which the wrong date was signed to the jurat and a different name signed to the complaint is not fatal, as the facts show it was made before the arrest and the trial of the defendant. 

Com. v. Soper, 133 Mass. 393

The complaint alleges that a certain complaint was made on the 3d of January 1888. The fact that it was made on the 31st of December 1881 is not a fatal variance. 

Com. v. Dacey, 107. Mass. 206

A robbery was alleged as of December 3, the evidence did not disclose on what day it was committed; but if it was proved to have been committed before the finding of the indictment it is admissible. It is not necessary to prove the precise day on which the crime is committed. 

Com. v. Doyle, 110 Mass. 103

A complaint which alleges that the defendant was guilty at a day then in the future alleges no offense as already having been committed and is void. 

Com. v. Campbell, 103 Mass. 436

By one of the familiar rules of evidence and criminal pleading the time which is required to be alleged need not be proved as laid. 

Com. v. Maloney, 112 Mass. 283

It is true that in criminal prosecutions the exact date need not necessarily be proved as laid, but every complaint must allege a precise date. 

Com. v. Adams, 1 Gray, 481

A complaint which alleges that the defendant on the 23d and on the 29th day of July did unlawfully sell liquor, etc.,

CRIMINAL CASES 

charges only one sale and is insufficient in that it does not state the time with sufficient certainty. 

Ownership of property. 

Com. v. Crowley, 167 Mass. 434

In complaints for attempting to steal property the ownership need not be alleged. 

Com. v. Butts, 124 Mass. 449

A cashier of a bank in Abington was paid a sum of money by the cashier of a Boston bank for delivery to the bank in Abington. The complaint alleged the property to be that of the cashier and not the property of the bank. The pleader could allege that it was the property of either. 

Com. v. Weiner, 255 Mass. 506

If goods are in the possession and custody of a clerk in a store and he is attacked and robbed of them, the indictment which alleges the ownership of the goods in the true owner is good. It could have been alleged as the property of either the clerk or the true owner. 

Com. v. Bennett, 118 Mass. 443

An allegation that the defendant had taken money of the said A is a sufficient allegation that it was the money and property of A. Certain moneys of the amount and value of ...... dollars sufficiently describes the property. 

Com. v. Dailey, 110 Mass. 503

A husband hired a building, and although he deserted his family, he is still in the possession so far as alleging it as his property on a complaint for breaking and entering. 

Com. v. Finn, 108 Mass. 466

In an indictment for receiving stolen property the pleader may aver the person from whom it was stolen, although he stole it himself from the true owner. 

Com. v. Maguire, 108 Mass. 469

If goods belonging to several people are jointly stolen from the custody of one of them in whose possession they had been put for sale, the indictment may aver that they were his property.

CRIMINAL CASES 

395 

Com. v. Goldstein, 114 Mass. 272

In a complaint for burning insured property the ownership may be alleged to be the property of any member of the firm; it is not material in whose name the goods were insured. 

Com. v. McGorty, 114 Mass. 299

Property can be alleged as the property of the widow of the deceased. It was the property of the estate, but she was in the lawful possession. 

Com. v. Arrance, 5 Allen, 517 

Com. v. O'Brien, 12 Allen, 183

Joint owner: either may be alleged as tenants in common, husband and wife, constructive possession is enough; or it may be alleged as the property of either one of the partners. 

Com. v. McAvoy, 16 Gray, 235

A description of the building as the property of Nathan S. 

Hoard is not supported by proof of evidence of the property of Nathan Hoard. The names are not the same. Corporation. 

Com. v. Simonds, 11 Gray, 306

When a corporation is averred in a complaint, the pleader must allege that it is a corporation incorporated by the laws of Massachusetts, that is to say, "a corporation legally established." Overlapping. 

Com. v. Robinson, 126 Mass. 259 

Com. v. Dunster, 145 Mass. 101

An acquittal for a nuisance complaint pleaded continuando from January 1 to May 28 is a bar to a complaint for keeping the same place from January 1 to August 20 of the same year. 

Com. v. Fraher, 126 Mass. 265

If a plea in bar to a complaint which overlaps the first case has not been adjudicated, the plea in bar fails. 

Com. v. Goulet, 160 Mass. 276 

A complaint from July 1 to November 21. A similar offense on November 20. Even though the complaint was sworn to on

November 2, the offense is not an indivisible offense.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Connors, 116 Mass. 35

The first case charges an offense between July 2, 1872, and 

May 1, 1873. The second case charges an offense between May 1, 1873, and June 12, 1873. The second case is not a bar to the first, and the second does not overlap the first. 

Com. v. Cain, 14 Gray, 7 

From the 24th day of September to December 24th does not overlap a complaint from April 1 to September 24.

Com. v. Runge, 231 Mass. 598 

Com. v. Robinson, 126 Mass. 259 

Com. v. Anderson, 220 Mass. 142

Where the time is an essential element of a crime, like the offense of unlawfully practicing medicine, a single indivisible offense, and a part of the description of the offense charged is the duration of time during which it is charged the series of acts took place, evidence must be confined to the time between dates. 

Com. v. Peretz, 212 Mass. 253

An indictment which charges a continuing offense and a part covering it covers a time when there was not any law, is a good indictment. The part averred in which there is a law for the offense is good. 

Com. v. Mitchell, 258 Mass. 89

If a complaint charges a criminal offense covering a period of time during a part of which it would be properly charged, the fact that during another part of that period it would not legally be charged is not a defense. 

Com. v. Lord, 147 Mass. 399

On a complaint charging continuando it is sufficient if the defendant was guilty of some substantial portion of the time alleged. The complaint charged continually; the word "continually" could be rejected as surplusage. 

Com. v. Frates, 16 Gray, 236 "On the 1st day of August and from that day to the day of receiving this complaint" charges an offense during all of that time. The complaint was not dated, but the certificate of the justice bore the date of August 29.

CRIMINAL CASES 

397 

Com. v. Adams, 4 Gray, 27 "From a day named to the day of finding the presentment and filing of this indictment" is fatally defective. Indefinite, as it fixes no time. 

Com. v. Gardner, 7 Gray, 494 

On the 1st day of October, 1855, and on divers dates since.

Proof must be confined to acts done on October 1. 

Words of the statute. 

Com. v. Dyer, 128 Mass. 70

When an offense is created by statute which sets forth with precision and certainty all the elements of an offense, an indictment or complaint is sufficient which charges the offense in the words of the statute. 

Com. v. Snell, 189 Mass. 12

Words used in a statute to define a crime, or other words conveying the same meaning, may be used. 

Com. v. Dewhirst, 190 Mass. 293

It is enough if the crime is charged in the language of the statute creating it. 

Com. v. Danziger, 176 Mass. 290

It is a general rule sufficient to charge a crime in the words of the statute.

Against the peace. See Chapter 277, Section 330. 

Com. v. Stockbridge, 11 Mass. 279

It is not sufficient in an indictment for an offense created by statute to allege the same to have been committed against the law in such case made and provided. 

Com. v. Hoxey, 16 Mass. 385 

An indictment concluding "against the statute" may be maintained if the facts charged amount to an offense at common law, and are not within the purview of the statute. 

Com. v. Freelove, 150 Mass. 66

The omission of "against the peace" does not void the com plaint. (See Chapter 277, Section 33.) The legislature cannot authorize the omission of allegations necessary to describe a specific crime.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Hoye, 11 Gray, 462

An indictment on a public statute need not recite it or specially refer to it, but may conclude "against the peace." The court is bound to take notice of all public statutes and refer the acts which are charged in the indictment to such statutes as make them punishable. Junior. 

Com. v. Parmenter, 101 Mass. 211 

Simpson v. Dix, 131 Mass. 179 

The word "junior" is not part of a man's name. "The second of that name" is sufficient only for the purpose of designation and description. 

Second offense. 

Walsh v. Com., 224 Mass. 39

The second conviction of an offense must be specially pleaded. 

Tuttle v. Com., 2 Gray, 505

The second offense must not only be specially pleaded but must be proved. County. 

Com. v. Wheeler, 162 Mass. 429 

A complaint must specifically state in what county. 

Deemed to be larceny. 

Com. v. Tenney, 97 Mass. 50 

Com. v. Simpson, 9 Met. 138

A statutory offense must be pleaded in the words of the statute where the phraseology is "deems an offense to be larceny." 

Caption. 

Com. v. Hines, 101 Mass. 33 

Com. v. Stone, 3 Gray, 453

The caption does not form any part of an indictment, and any mistake may be rectified and amended. Shop. 

Com. v. Annis, 15 Gray, 197 The word "shop" included buildings where goods are sold by retail, and the word "store" is a place where goods are deposited

CRIMINAL CASES 

399 and stored. In this country shops for the sale of goods are frequently called stores.

Names of streets. 

Com. v. Noxon, 121 Mass. 42

Where a highway is known by two different names, it is not a variance to describe it by one of them. 

Com. v. Allen, 11 Met. 403

The defendant was charged with violation of the law of the road, and the name of the road was not averred. The defendant was sufficiently apprised of the charge and the indictment is sufficient. 

Com. v. Clancy, 261 Mass. 345

In a complaint for using a motor vehicle without authority the name of the way on which the defendant was operating must be pleaded. 

Com. v. Gurney, 261 Mass. 309

In a complaint for operating a motor vehicle so as to endanger, etc., the name of the way must be averred. Duplicity. 

Com. v. Eaton, 15 Pick. 273 

Com. v. Twitchell, 4 Cush. 74

Did offer for sale and did sell is not bad for duplicity; it charges only one offense. 

Com. v. Harney, 10 Met. 422

An averment that A did willfully set fire to and burn a dwelling house and in such attempt did break and enter a certain house and did then and there procure shavings, etc., is not bad for duplicity. 

Com. v. Stowell, 9 Met. 569 

Com. v. Odlin, 23 Pick. 275

Did presume to be a seller and did sell, etc., is not bad for duplicity; the presume part can be rejected as surplusage. 

Erasures and interlineations. 

Com. v. Fagan, 15 Gray, 194

Erasures and interlineations in an indictment without any note or certificate to show when or by whom they were made affords no ground for arresting judgment.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Desmarteau, 16 Gray at 16

The use of the abbreviation sd. for said, and interlineations in an indictment will furnish a proper ground for a motion to quash if made before a plea has been taken. 

Words "or" and "and." 

Com. v. Twitchell, 4 Cush. 74

The statute says "set up or promote a lottery," etc. The indictment was drawn "set up and promote a lottery," etc. Not bad for duplicity. 

Com. v. Mara, 257 Mass. 198

In a complaint for operating a motor vehicle so as to endanger, etc., the statute says "lives or safety of the public." The complaint alleged "lives and safety." Not bad for duplicity. 

Year of our Lord. 

Com. v. Doran, 14 Gray, 37 "The year of our Lord" omitted in a complaint referring to the date of the crime is not a fatal defect. 

Force and arms. 

Com. v. Crowley, 167 Mass. 434 "Force and arms" is not sufficient pleading if the statute avers "force and violence." Now cured by Chapter 277, Section 33,

General Laws. 

Where the penalty goes to the informer. 

Com. v. Baker, 2 Gray, 78

Where the penalty or a part of it is given to the informer, his name must be stated in the information. 

Description of property 

- persons phraseology. 

Com. v. Hodgkins, 170 Mass. 197 

A complaint charging the possession of dead lobsters is not a variance if the evidence showed possession of live ones. 

Com. v. Beaman, 8 Gray, 497 

Live animals- larceny. The law presumes them to be alive, and if they are dead, it must be so pleaded. 

Com. v. Peakes, 231 Mass. 449 

A receipt is an accountable receipt for money.
