401 

Com. v. O'Connell, 12 Allen, 451

The statement of the aggregate value of the property stolen where all the articles are of one kind is sufficient. 

Com. v. Lavery, 101 Mass. 207

Larceny of twelve handkerchiefs of the value of six dollars is fatal. 

Com. v. Hall, 15 Mass. 240

A complaint alleging that A erected a number of sheds, without stating the exact number, is fatal. 

Com. v. Smith, 1 Mass. 245

In an indictment for stealing money the value must be averred. 

Com. v. Boynton, 12 Cush. 499

The indictment for selling diseased and corrupted food must distinctly aver the vendor knew that they were diseased. An allegation that he did knowingly sell is not sufficient. 

Com. v. Concannon, 5 Allen, 502 

Description of the larceny of a mortgage deed. 

Com. v. Smith, 153 Mass. 97 

The day of the month in Arabic numerals is good — 15th day of July 1855. 

Com. v. Graustein,.209 Mass. 38

An averment that a certain foreign substance had been added to milk, a further description of the same being unknown to the complainant, was pleaded and the fact that the substance was known is not a variance. 

Com. v. Cabot, 241 Mass. 131

One piece of paper, the same being a deed, is good. It need not have any exaggerated value but it must have some value even if it is intrinsic and the value must be averred. 

Com. v. McLoon, 5 Gray, 91

An indictment or complaint which states the year of the commission of the offense in figures only without prefixing the letters 

A.D. is insufficient. 

Com. v. Prescott, 153 Mass. 396 

Did unlawfully take 1000 smelts is good, it being one offense.

The number is pleaded for the purpose of fixing the penalty.

CRIMINAL CASES Com. v. Kern, 147 Mass. 595 

When an offense is local in its nature, the allegation of the place is a necessary part of the description of the offense, and must be proved as laid. 

Com. v. Clark, 145 Mass. 251

If the complaint covers a period of more than six months, it is not open to an objection. 

Com. v. Shaw, 145 Mass. 349 "The larceny of 1320 pairs of shoes, each pair of the value of one dollar." The word "shoes" must be taken as shoes for the human feet. 

Com. v. Gedzium, 259 Mass. 453

On a John Doe indictment the true name can subsequently be inserted; it is not an amendment. 

Fitzgerald v. Com., 135 Mass. 266

The first three counts larceny by trick against three defendants, the last three counts against one defendant as principal and accessory before and after the fact against the other two. Can charge one check and order for the payment of money and one piece of paper in one count. 

Com. v. Philpot, 130 Mass. 59

If the word "feloniously" be used in an indictment, it can be rejected as surplusage. 

Com. v. Gallagher, 126 Mass. 54 "Divers promissory notes current as money in said commonwealth" is a sufficient pleading. Bank bills may be alleged as promissory notes. 

Com. v. Chase, 125 Mass. 202

The word "confectionery" is a generic word and does not properly describe the substance which the defendant is charged with adulterating, with the precision and certainty that the Constitution and rules of pleading require. 

Com. v. Martin, 125 Mass. 394

An averment that the defendant committed the crime in some way and manner and by some means, instruments, and weapons unknown is sufficient.

CRIMINAL CASES 

403 

Com. v. Green, 122 Mass. 333 

An indictment charged the robbery of divers promissory notes current as money in said commonwealth; the proof was three tens, eleven fives, and one two. There was not any variance, though the grand jury knew the description before the indictment was drawn. 

Com. v. Gavin, 121 Mass. 54 

Every allegation, whether it be necessary or unnecessary and whether it be more or less particular, which is descriptive of the identity of that which is legally essential to the charge in the indictment, must be proved strictly and with exactness. 

Com. v. Washburn, 128 Mass. 421

A complaint which does not set forth the facts necessary to constitute an offense except by reference to a statute, the year of which is wrongly given, will not support a conviction, and the objection may be taken for the first time at the trial in the Superior Court on an appeal. 

Com. v. Hagerman, 10 Allen, 401

A jurat expressed in figures is good, but the better pleading should be expressed in the English language. 

Com. v. McCan, 277 Mass. 199

In a complaint for assault and battery, the pleader need not allege the acts of indecency. 

Com. v. Clark, 4 Cush. 595 

The words A.D. preceding the year and the & sign for "and" may be used. 

Com. v. Pray, 13 Pick. 359

An indictment for being a common thief is not sufficient; must be averred why. 

Com. v. Burke, 14 Gray, 100

Matters of aggravation are not a descriptive element and when alleged affirmatively charging an assault with an aggravation, the defendant may be convicted of the substantive charge without the aggravation. 

Com. v. Parmenter, 5 Pick. 279

According to the purport and effect, the pleader need not set out the exact language and written matter, but if he pleads of

CRIMINAL CASES 

the tenor following, he must allege the identical words and matter. 

Com. v. Herrick, 255 Mass. 510

Pleading accessory. From an early time it seems to have been the practice to charge either in separate indictments or in different counts of a single indictment that the same person was principal and accessory before the fact to a. known or unknown principal, and in the accessory complaint the pleader can plead that the principal is unknown. 

Com. v. Rice, 9 Met. 253 

In the phrase "with cart, wagon, sleigh and otherwise" for the purpose of vending, etc., the word "otherwise" does not include things. Jusdem generis. 

Com. v. Caldwell, 14 Mass. 330

In refusing to answer to a tithingman the pleader need not allege that he was duly sworn nor that he had any wand or badge of office, nor that he was known to the defendant as a tithingman. 

Com. v. Hunt, 4 Pick. 252

The indictment alleged "Albert Hunt in and upon Peddy Harvey did make an assault and her the said Peddy Hunt did beat, wound and evil treat with intent her the said Peddy Harvey to ravish." The clause "her the said Peddy Hunt did then and there beat, wound and evil treat" can be rejected as surplusage. 

Com. v. Stearns, 10 Met. 256 

Edwards v. Com., 19 Pick. 124

In pleading the possession of ten or more counterfeit bills, etc., it is necessary to aver that the party accused had them in his possession at the same time. An averment that he had them in his possession on the same day is not sufficient. 

Com. v. Bolkum, 3 Pick. 281

A complaint which alleges that A, being licensed as an inn holder, did allow cards to be played is not good; the averment must be that he actually kept such inn. 

Joinder of counts. 

Com. v. Kimball, 7 Gray, 328

Keeping a noisy and disorderly house and a nuisance of ill fame may be joined.

CRIMINAL CASES 

405 

Com. v. Wetherhead, 110 Mass. 175

Two persons fishing in one boat. Although on separate account, could properly be charged in the same complaint. 

Com. v. Jacobs, 152 Mass. 276

In this state several different substantive crimes may be included in different counts of the same indictment, when they are of the same general nature, and when the mode of trial and the nature of the punishment are the same. If there is an enjoinder, the cure is to make a finding for the defendant on all but one count. 

Com. v. Riseman, 257 Mass. 254 

Com. v. Dyer, 243 Mass. 472

A felony and a misdemeanor may be joined in the same indictment if the crimes are of a kindred nature and the punishment of the same character. 

Com. v. Ismahl, 134 Mass. 201

A complaint for a house of ill fame and a disorderly house can be joined, and it need not be pleaded that they are different descriptions of the same criminal act. The defendant is not entitled to require the state to elect on which count it will go to the jury, although the evidence discloses but one series of facts or transactions. 

Com. v. Darling, 129 Mass. 112

It is no misjoinder to charge in the same indictment either in one or several counts one person with breaking and entering a building and stealing therein, and another person with receiving stolen goods. 

Pettees v. Com., 126 Mass. 242

In the first count the defendant is charged with forgery. In the second count it charged one B with uttering, and in the third count charged the defendant with being an accessory before the fact to the uttering. Held that the complaint contained only two counts. 

Com. v. Ballou, 124 Mass. 26 

Com. v. Howe, 13 Gray, 26

A complaint which alleges that the defendant kept a dwelling house used for prostitution, illegal gaming, and exposing liquor is not bad by reason of duplicity.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Hollender, 200 Mass. 73

Pleading of perjury in two counts, no allegation that they were different descriptions of the same act, must be taken as one count. 

Com. v. Crossley, 162 Mass. 515

Each count must be sufficient in itself, and an averment in one count cannot correct and aid defects in another. The exception is, the pleader can say in one count referring to the preceding one, and the said complainant, etc. 

Com. v. Smith, 11 Mass. 456 

Several offenses may be set forth in one complaint. A statute which describes the form of complaint does not require that it should be literally pursued, but only that the complaint should set forth substantially as prescribed. 

Com. v. Lowrey, 159 Mass. 62

Defendant is charged in three counts in one indictment. 

Alleged to have been committed on the same day and the same place. No allegations of different description of the same offense. 

A record in a criminal case is to be construed in favor of the legality of the proceedings by refusing to draw from it any unnecessary presumption impugning their validity. Defendant was found guilty on one and not guilty on the others. 

Com. v. Galligan, 155 Mass. 54 

First count, a liquor nuisance; second, third, and fourth counts, illegal sales. Can be joined. 

Joinder of defendants. 

Com. v. Wetherhead, 110 Mass. 175

Two persons were unlawfully fishing on a great pond at the same time from the same boat; they may be joined as defendants although each was fishing on his own account. 

Com. v. Knapp, 9 Pick. 496

A person who is present and aiding in the commission of the crime by another and is thus a principal in the second degree, may be convicted on an indictment charging him with having committed the crime. 

Com. v. Roberts, 108 Mass. 296

Three defendants jointly charged with manslaughter with a knife may be convicted although only one of them used the

CRIMINAL CASES 

407 knife; the others were present aiding and abetting or may be found guilty in different degrees. 

Com. v. Bailey, 13 Allen, 541 

Com. v. Sampson, 97 Mass. 407

An indictment may be maintained against two fishermen for jointly taking clams for bait, if they belonged to the same vessel.

Surplusage. 

Com. v. Wellington, 7 Allen, 299

No allegation whether it be necessary or unnecessary, whether it be less or more particular, which is descriptive of the identity of that which is legally essential to the charge in the indictment can ever be rejected as surplusage. 

Com. v. Hartwell, 128 Mass. 415

When an averment can be rejected as surplusage. When not descriptive of the charge, or of anything essential to it, when an indictment alleges the commission of an offense by various means, it is sufficient to prove enough of the means to constitute the offense as in false pretenses, but when an indictment states a pretense in general terms and then specifies the particulars, it is the particular and not the general which must be proved.

Different descriptions of the same offense. 

Com. v. Holmes, 103 Mass. 440

The omission in an indictment to aver that they are different descriptions of the same offense. The difficulty, if any, is en- tirely relieved by a verdict of not guilty on one count and a nol pros of the other. 

Idem sonans. 

Com. v. Stone, 103 Mass. 421

The assaulted party was referred to as Catherine Marres and averred as such. She testified that her name was Catherine Mars, pronounced in one syllable. It is sufficient to sustain the averment of a name charged if the jury find that the name, although differently spelled, is substantially identical in pronunciation.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Unknown person. 

Com. v. Sherman, 13 Allen, 248 

Com. v. Stoddard, 9 Allen, 28

If the name of the person whom it is necessary to refer to in a complaint is unknown to the complainant, it may be so alleged although he might easily have ascertained the same. 

Referring to the "said" erroneously. 

Com. v. Hagerman, 10 Allen, 401 

The complainant's name was John Smith, the defendant's name was John Hagerman; in the second count the pleader referred to and described the defendant as the "said John"; this does not void the complaint.

Of the tenor following. 

Com. v. Clancy, 7 Allen, 537

When the pleader alleges "of the tenor following, that is to say" it means a full and exact description of the instrument to be set out or the language. 

Com. v. Parmenter, 5 Pick. 279

According to the purport and effect, the pleader need not set out the exact language nor written matter, but if he pleads of the tenor following, he must set out the identical words and matters. 

The word "or." 

Com. v. Grey, 2 Gray, 501 (see 4 Cush. 74)

A complaint which charges A sold intoxicating or spirituous liquors is fatal. An averment that A sold intoxicating and spirituous liquors is good, and proof that the defendant sold either would be good. Unlawfully. 

Com. v. Byrnes, 126 Mass. 248 

The insertion of the word "unlawfully" in a complaint does not dispense with the necessity of specifically alleging the elements required by the enacting clause of the statute and by the rules of criminal pleading to constitute the crime charged against the defendant.

CRIMINAL CASES 

409 Intent. 

Com. v. Boynton, 12 Cush. 499

Wherever the intention of the party is necessary to constitute an offense such intent must be alleged in every material part of the description where it so constitutes it. 

Series of acts. 

Com. v. Adams, 4 Gray, 27 

Com. v. Runge, 231 Mass. 598

Where the offense consists of a series of acts which taken together constitute a criminal practice or occupation, time enters into the essence of the offense, fixes its identity, and is a material element in its description. It must be alleged with certainty, and proof must be confined to acts done within the time charged. 

Phrase "that day." 

Com. v. McKenney, 14 Gray, 1

The phrase "that day" means the day last named. The clause "she not being then and there duly appointed" refers to the time set forth in the indictment pleaded continuando. 

Punishment contingent on the pleading. 

Com. v. Maxwell, 2 Pick. 139

Where the penalty provides for a fine for each person illegally entertained at an inn the complaint must allege specifically how many were entertained.

PLEADINGS UNDER THE DIFFERENT CRIMES 

See also under respective heads.

Abortion. 

Com. v. Hartford, 193 Mass. 464

A complaint charging that the defendant did knowingly distribute circulars relative to the procuring of an abortion is sufficient, and a general averment that the defendant committed the acts; and no further averment of a guilty knowledge of the contents is unnecessary.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Wagner, 231 Mass. 265 

Com. v. Sinclair, 195 Mass. 100 

Form of complaint where death ensued. 

Com. v. Homer, 153 Mass. 343 

Com. v. Adams, 127 Mass. 15

The death of the woman need not necessarily be pleaded. The fact of her death affects the punishment only. 

Com. v. Hebert, 264 Mass. 571

The complaint charged an abortion by means of a certain instrument. The fact that the averment was not made "the name of the instrument being unknown" is not bad for duplicity, and a motion to quash will not lie. A bill of particulars alleging that the state could not describe the instrument used, conformed to the law. 

Com. v. Donoghue, 266 Mass. 391

Not naming the instrument and not pleading the name unknown is cured by a bill of particulars. 

Com. v. Taylor, 132 Mass. 261 

Com. v. Surles, 165 Mass. 59

In an indictment for abortion the fact that she was pregnant does not have to be pleaded. If the fact that she was pregnant is pleaded, that fact must be proved. 

Com. v. Snow, 116 Mass. 47

The date of the crime is not immaterial. 

Com. v. Sholes, 13 Allen, 554

An indictment need not allege that the act was done maliciously and without lawful justification; it is sufficient to allege that it was unlawfully done with the intent to procure and cause a miscarriage. 

Com. v. Morrison, 16 Gray, 224

In an indictment for causing a woman to take medicine the pleader need not aver or prove the name of the medicine or that it was noxious. The only overt act to be proved is the advice to her to take the medicine. Adultery. 

Com. v. Fuller, 163 Mass. 499

Must be charged as a single offense, cannot be pleaded continuando.

CRIMINAL CASES 

411 

Com. v. Cobb, 14 Gray, 57 

Com. v. Merriam, 14 Pick. 518

A day must be alleged in the complaint but the state need not prove that the crime was committed on the very day alleged. 

If the proof shows that the act was committed on any day within the period fixed by the statute of limitations, it is sufficient. 

Com. v. Tompson, 2 Cush. 551

If the averment charges intercourse with a woman, she not being the lawful wife of the defendant, it is a sufficient averment. 

Com. v. Reardon, 6 Cush. 78

If the complaint against the man avers adultery with a married woman, not the lawful wife of the defendant, it is not necessary to allege that the defendant was married or unmarried. If the complaint alleges the woman was the wife of another man this allegation is sufficient. 

Moore v. Com., 6 Met, 243

The complaint must allege that the man and woman were not married to each other. Any form which charges she was the wife of somebody else other than the accused is sufficient. 

Com. v. Fuller, 163 Mass. 499

Adultery must be pleaded as a single offense. 

Arson. 

Com. v. Hayden, 150 Mass. 332

If the complaint alleges the burning of a dwelling house, it must be proved as laid. 

Com. v. Uhrig, 167 Mass. 420 

Form of complaint for burning a warehouse. If the allegation is in the night time, this can be rejected as surplusage. 

Com. v. Cooper, 264 Mass. 378 

Com. v. Jaffas, 284 Mass. 417 

In a complaint for arson the words "maliciously” and “willfully" must be pleaded. Not to do so is fatal and cannot be cured by Section 33 of Chapter 277 of the General Laws. 

Com. v. Hamilton, 15 Gray, 480

A complaint for burning a building need not contain an allegation of its value unless the punishment depends on the value.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Mahar, 33 Mass. 120

The name of the owner of the dwelling house might be alleged. 

Com. v. Squire, 1 Met. 258 

Com. v. Tucker, 110 Mass. 403

The term "building" need not represent a finished structure and perfect for the use for which it is intended to be used. 

Com. v. Barney, 10 Cush. 478 

Must be a dwelling house for the habitation of man. 

Com. v. Horrigan, 2 Allen, 159

A building which was removed and afterwards fitted up as a schoolhouse and engine house will support an allegation that the building was erected for public use. The removal of a building and fitting it up for occupancy is "erecting." 

Com. v. Jaffas, 284 Mass. 417

The indictment, reading "did willfully and maliciously aid, counsel and assist in an attempt, etc." to set fire to or burn a building, was not defective in that it omitted the words "willfully and maliciously" as a description of the attempt "to set fire to or burn." The words "did counsel, aid and assist" were superfluous and unnecessary, the crime being a misdemeanor, and might be rejected as surplusage. The gravaman of the offense is that the defendant participated, and the fact that he was only a part owner was not a defense. 

Assault and battery with intent to maim. 

Com. v. Blaney, 133 Mass. 571

In a complaint for assault and battery with intent to maim, the aggravation was not well alleged, but the complaint charged a formal assault and battery, and could not be quashed or adjudged void on a demurrer. 

Assault and battery with intent to murder. 

Com. v. Demboski, 283 Mass. 316

One indicted for assault and battery with intent to murder may be convicted of assault and battery with intent to kill. He is found guilty with intent to commit manslaughter, punishable under Section 29 of Chapter 265 of the General Laws. Intent to kill and intent to murder are different.

CRIMINAL CASES 

413 

Com. v. Bearse, 108 Mass. 487

In an indictment for mingling poison with food the pleader need not allege that the defendant knew that it was poison or did the act knowingly, or that the woman was about to eat the food, or that he intended that she should eat it. 

Com. v. Galavan, 9 Allen, 271

An indictment for mingling poison with food and drink need not contain an averment that the mixture was poisonous or that the defendant knew it to be so. 

Com. v. Hersey, 2 Allen, 173

It is not necessary to allege in the complaint that the poison was administered with intent to kill. 

Assault and battery with intent to rescue. 

Com. v. Filburn, 119 Mass. 297

The pleader should allege that the taking out of the custody of the police officer was unlawful, to offset the fact that the aiding might be innocent. 

Com. v. Malloy, 119 Mass. 347

In a complaint for assault and battery with intent to rescue it is not necessary to allege that the defendant knew that the prisoner was in the custody of the officer and need not allege that the drunkenness was in the presence of the officer. 

Com. v. Lee, 107 Mass. 207

An indictment need not allege process on which the prisoner was held and the nature and circumstances of the holding. The fact that the prisoner was held in custody under a lawful arrest was the essential matter to be proved. 

Assault and battery with a pistol. 

Com. v. Fenno, 125 Mass. 387 

An allegation that AB, with a dangerous weapon, to wit, "with a pistol loaded with gunpowder and leaden bullets did make an assault," is a sufficient description. It is never necessary to set forth the means with which the defendant intended to effect the murder. 

Com. v. Morgan, 280 Mass. 392 

A complaint charging an assault with a dangerous weapon does not fall within the provisions of Section 15A of Chapter

CRIMINAL CASES 

265 of the General Laws. That section charges an assault by means of a dangerous weapon, which is a felony, the former being only a misdemeanor. 

Assault and battery - indecent. 

Com. v. McCan, 277 Mass. 199

In a complaint for indecent assault and battery it is not necessary to allege the indecency. 

Assault and battery on police officer on duty. 

Com. v. Kirby, 2 Cush. 577

The pleader must aver that the defendant knew that he was a police officer. 

Attempt to commit a crime. 

Com. v. Cline, 213 Mass. 225

In a complaint for attempting to commit the crime of larceny from the person the complaint need not be set forth that there was any property in the pocket. 

Com. v. Crowley, 167 Mass. 434

A charge of attempt must be set out in direct terms. 

Com. v. Koslowsky, 238 Mass. 379

Attempt to steal an automobile. The short form upheld. The cure is by a bill of particulars. 

Com. v. Shedd, 140 Mass. 451

Form of complaint for attempting to break and enter a dwelling house with intent to steal. Automobile. 

Com. v. Clancy, 261 Mass. 345 

Using an automobile without authority: the complaint must set forth the name of the street he was operating on. 

Com. v. Gurney, 261 Mass. 309

Operating so as to endanger: the name of the street on which the automobile was being operated must be set out. 

Com. v. Mara, 257 Mass. 198 

Operating so as to endanger: the words of the statute are "operating so that the lives and safety of the public," etc. The

CRIMINAL CASES 

415 pleader pleaded "the lives or safety of the public," etc. Held good. 

Com. v. Massad, 242 Mass. 532

The complaint did not state the name of the person collided with. Evidence as to who was the injured party was admissible, and that she was struck. 

Com. v. Bleakney, 278 Mass. 198

In pleading a complaint for going away after colliding with another car or person, the complaint alleges "did knowingly go away after colliding with a certain person." The clause "or otherwise injuring" contemplates the causing of the injury. 

Com. v. Allen, 11 Met. 403

On a complaint against a traveler for not driving his carriage to the right of the middle of the traveled part of a road, it is not necessary to set forth a particular description of the road. 

Breaking and entering. 

Com. v. Bowden, 14 Gray, 103

A certain building there situate, to wit, the shop of AB, is supported by proof that AB occupied only a room in the building of several rooms. 

Com. v. Thompson, 9 Gray, 108

Where a shop is occupied by two persons as tenants in common, the shop may be described as the property of either. 

Com. v. Reynolds, 122 Mass. 454

In a complaint for breaking and entering, a dwelling house of A is sustained by proof of the breaking and entering a house owned by A but not occupied. A had constructive possession. It is only for identification merely and not to give a legal character to it. 

Com. v. McMonagle, 1 Mass. 517

Complaint for breaking and entering a store cannot be supported unless the allegation is that the store is a building. 

Com. v. White, 6 Cush. 181

The passenger room of a railroad station having within it a separate enclosed room where books and tickets are sold is not an office.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Moriarty, 135 Mass. 540

A certain building, to wit, the office building of a corporation named. At the trial, evidence was introduced that there were other office buildings of the same corporation in the same jurisdiction. Complaint was good and accurately described the building. 

Com. v. Moore, 130 Mass. 45

A complaint that charged that A broke and entered the building of B with the intent to steal the goods of B is not supported by proof that A intends to steal the property of C. The pleader can allege an intent to break and enter and to commit the crime of larceny generally. 

Com. v. Grace, 265 Mass. 119

The amount of property stolen is immaterial in the prosecution for breaking and entering in the night time with intent to steal. 

Com. v. Perris, 108 Mass. 1

In an indictment for breaking and entering a building with intent to commit a felony, the ownership of the building must be alleged or the complaint is void. 

Com. v. Doherty, 10 Cush. 52

In an indictment it need not be alleged that the defendant "then and there intended to commit rape" nor need the offense of rape be fully and technically set forth. Did break and enter a building, to wit, the dwelling house, to commit rape. 

Com. v. Williams, 2 Cush. 582

Breaking and entering a building and stealing therein, the amount of the property stolen is immaterial. Bribery. 

Com. v. Milliken, 174 Mass. 79

The complaint need not contain fully the agreement under which the money was received by a juror where the complaint followed the words of the statute.

Being armed when arrested upon a warrant. 

Com. v. Doherty, 103 Mass. 443

There must be an allegation in the complaint that the arrest was lawful.

CRIMINAL CASES 

417 

Burglar's tools. 

Com. v. Conlin, 188 Mass. 282

Two defendants can be joined in one complaint. The tools were found in the room to which the defendant had the key. It was constructive possession. 

Common and notorious thief. 

Rice v. Com., 12 Met. 246 

Com. v. Pray, 13 Pick. 359

An indictment for being a common and notorious thief, the pleader must allege why. Discussions and pleadings for a. 

Conspiracy. 

Com. v. Barnes, 132 Mass. 242

In an indictment for conspiracy the pleader must allege an unlawful purpose. 

Com. v. Dyer, 243 Mass. 472

All the conspirators need not be named in the indictment. 

Com. v. Harris, 231 Mass. 584

A conspiracy to receive stolen goods must be pleaded continuando, a conspiracy to do an unlawful act is complete when the conspiracy is made, before any act is done in pursuance of it. 

Com. v. Fuller, 132 Mass. 563

The indictment need not allege any actual injury to the person intended to be defrauded, or any denial of the truth of the various false pretenses; it must set out the intention to cheat some certain person, the conspiracy to do so, the property out of what he was to be cheated, and the means by which the conspiracy was to be accomplished. 

Com. v. McCarthy, 272 Mass. 107 

A complaint alleged a conspiracy on the 15th day of August.

The bill of particulars stated that the conspiracy was formed on or about that date, evidence that in the latter part of June or the first of July was admitted. Form of complaint for conspiracy in the future. 

Com. v. Walker, 108 Mass. 309

A conspiracy to obtain by means of false pretenses goods on credit need not allege a complete description of the goods, to obtain them generally is within the statute. Form of complaint.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Wallace, 16 Gray, 221 

If the means are by cheat, false pretenses, or fraud, the facts must be set forth. Form of complaint for. 

Com. v. Shedd, 7 Cush. 514

The pleader must allege the means intended to be used. 

Com. v. Eastman, 1 Cush. 189

When a conspiracy is plainly and technically set forth, the acts done in pursuance of it need not be set out, or if set out need not be proved. 

Com. v. Tibbetts, 2 Mass. 536

In a conspiracy to accuse one of a crime it is not necessary to allege that the defendants procured or intended to procure an indictment or other legal process. 

Com. v. Riseman, 257 Mass. 254

A misdemeanor and a felony can be joined in one complaint. 

Com. v. Meserve, 154 Mass. 64

It is not a variance if some of the contemplated means are not proved as alleged, provided any criminal or unlawful means which are alleged are proved. 

Com. v. Dyer, 243 Mass. 472

In an indictment for conspiracy a general description of the illegal means employed by the defendant by terms of recognized meaning in law is sufficient, without the particularity which might be necessary in an indictment for a substantive crime. It is not open to the objection that it is vague. 

Concealing mortgaged property. 

Com. v. Wallace, 108 Mass. 12

An averment which charges in one count with removing and concealing and aiding and abetting in removing and concealing mortgaged property, the averment of aiding and abetting is superfluous. The defendant may be convicted that he removed and concealed the property upon proof that he abetted in so doing. 

Com. v. Strangford, 112 Mass. 289

The complaint which alleged and described the articles as a large quantity of readymade clothing of the value of $500, a large quantity of boots and shoes a more particular description being

CRIMINAL CASES 

419 unknown, is not void or fatal, as the value does not decide the penalty as in a larceny case.

Conveying encumbered real estate without notifying purchaser. 

Com. v. Brown, 15 Gray, 189

The complaint which describes the parcel as "a certain parcel of real estate situated in Salem in the County of Essex" is bad pleading. A certain deed of warranty is not sustained by proof of the conveyance of all the defendant's right, title, and interest subject to one mortgage. 

Cruelty to animals. 

Com. v. Flanagan, 137 Mass. 560 "Did cruelly drive a certain horse" is a good complaint. 

Com. v. Cornell, 213 Mass. 135 

Exposing poison so that cattle should swallow it is a good complaint. "Cattle" is not too general a term. 

Com. v. Porter, 164 Mass. 576

An allegation "did then and there cruelly drive" is sufficient. 

An allegation that the defendant knew that the horse was unfit for labor is unnecessary. 

Com. v. Curry, 150 Mass. 509

Failing to provide shelter and food is a different allegation than inflicting unnecessary cruelty upon an animal. 

Com. v. Lufkin, 7 Allen, 579

The general expression "animal," employed in the statute, would be insufficient in a complaint, without an averment of the specific animal overdriven. 

Com. v. Sowle, 9 Gray, 304

It has been held that in proceedings under a statute punishing the willful and malicious killing of the beasts of another person it is unnecessary to set forth in the complaint the mode of killing, and that the statutory words are sufficient. 

Com. v. Whitman, 118 Mass. 458

The pleader need not allege the ownership of the animal or that the cruelty was unnecessary nor that he had the charge and custody of the animal.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Thornton, 113 Mass. 457

Having the charge and custody of a certain animal, etc., did knowingly and willfully authorize and permit the same to be subjected to unnecessary torture, is not bad for ambiguity. 

Com. v. Lufkin, 7 Allen, 579 

The meaning of the word "cruel." 

Disorderly house, keeping a. 

Com. v. Bulman, 118 Mass. 456

A single room can be complained of for keeping a noisy and disorderly house. 

Com. v. Wise, 110 Mass. 181

A complaint charging the defendant with keeping a noisy and disorderly tenement is void, because tenement, although it includes a house and building, has a much more enlarged signification. It signifies land or any corporeal inheritance or anything of a permanent nature which may be holden. Dogs. 

Com. v. Coates, 169 Mass. 354

A complaint for keeping an unlicensed dog may be pleaded continuando. Drunkenness. 

Com. v. McNamara, 116 Mass. 340

The form for "drunkenness" in the allegation was "guilty of the crime of drunkenness." Is a good allegation but not one to be commended. Drugs. 

Com. v. Kane, 173 Mass. 477

The pleader need not set forth in the complaint any intent for the offense of being present when implements were found. 

Election laws. 

Com. v. Desmond, 122 Mass. 12

In a complaint for illegal voting the pleader must set out the fact that a meeting of the voters for the elections of certain officers was legally held at, etc.

CRIMINAL CASES 

421 

Extortion. 

Com. v. Buckley, 145 Mass. 181

A complaint for threatening to accuse Frank E. White of a crime is not sustained by proof that the person threatened was Frank A. White, there being no evidence that he was ever called 

Frank E. White. 

Com. v. Andrews, 132 Mass. 263

An indictment charging a conspiracy to extort money without alleging from whom the money was to be extorted cannot be sustained. 

Com. v. Philpot, 130 Mass. 59

If the exact words are not set out, their substance being stated with sufficient certainty to comply with the provisions of the statute, it is sufficient. 

Com. v. Snow, 269 Mass. 598

The name of the person threatened must be specified in the complaint. It is not necessary to specify therein the name of the person against whom the injury is threatened; the wording of the statute may be followed and the person described as “another." An allegation threatening Nora Downes is good if the name is Nora C. Downes. 

Robinson v. Com., 101 Mass. 27

There must be an averment that the threats were made verbally or by a written or printed communication. 

Com. v. Moulton, 108 Mass. 307

The word "thereby" is superfluous and need not be pleaded; it adds nothing to the meaning. 

Com. v. Pellegrini, 283 Mass. 300

An indictment charging that the defendant by a written or printed communication did maliciously threaten an injury to the person or property of another with intent thereby to extort money from a named person is sufficient, although the name of the individual whose person or property is threatened is not set out. The essential factors in such cases are: (1) malicious threat, (2) made to a named person, (3) of personal injury to someone, (4) with intent to extort money. The statute requires simply that the person threatened be "another." In this case a letter in Italian was left at a certain house where the person named lived.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Forgery. 

Com. v. Clancy, 7 Allen, 537

In setting out "passing as true an uncurrent bank bill," the omission in the complaint of the cashier's and president's name is fatal, Com. v. Hall, 97 Mass. 570

Uttering a counterfeit bank bill, form of complaint for. 

Com. v. Wilson, 2 Gray, 70

It is a fatal variance in a complaint for uttering a forgery to omit the name of the state in the upper margin of the bill if not repeated in the body thereof. Words "State of Maine" are part of the date and so part of the contract. 

Com. v. Taylor, 5 Cush. 605

The number and check letter of a forged instrument and the words in the margin need not be alleged in the complaint. 

Com. v. Houghton, 8 Mass. 107

The pleader must describe the counterfeit bills or set forth a sufficient reason for not doing so. 

Com. v. Atwood, 11 Mass. 93

Words in an indictment which may have been the means of a verdict cannot be rejected as surplusage to support a conviction. 

Com. v. Ladd, 15 Mass. 526

It is not necessary to allege that the goods were delivered in accordance with an acquittance; the false making with intent to defraud is the gist of the offense. 

Com. v. Stevens, 1 Mass. 203 

Com. v. Bailey, 1 Mass. 62

The word "tenor" binds to a strict recital, but the number of the bank bill and the words at the top, figures in the margin expressing the amount, are not part of the bill and need not be alleged; only the evidence of the contract need be set out. 

Com. v. Snow, 1 Mass. 54

Alleging the instrument to be "in the words and figures following," a strict recital is essential. 

Com. v. Ross, 2 Mass. 373 

A complaint for uttering a promissory note need not set forth the date of the note nor the time when the money is made payable.

CRIMINAL CASES 

423 

Com. v. Goodsmith, 249 Mass. 159

A description of what a stock certificate is. 

Com. v. Dunleavey, 157 Mass. 386 

A complaint must aver a false making for the purposes of fraud, also that the forged instrument is of such a description that it might defraud or deceive if used with that intent; if it does not appear on the surface the pleader must allege matter as would enable the court judicially to see the tendency. 

Com. v. Spillman, 124 Mass. 327

In pleading it is necessary that the words "alleged to be forged" be shown or averred to have such a relation to the contract as to be the subject of forgery; it is necessary to show affirmatively that the words written, as written, became a part of the instrument which is the subject of forgery. 

Com. v. Hinds, 101 Mass. 209

In pleading forgery at common law the fraud must be particularly pleaded to show that a crime has been committed. The instrument in itself does not show on its face that it is a forgery. 

Com. v. Ward, 2 Mass. 397

An indictment for forging a note need not allege the endorsement of the note; though it be forged, it is no part of the note. 

Com. v. Bailey, 199 Mass. 583

A description of the instrument need not be set forth. "A certain forged instrument purporting to be a promissory note" is sufficient. Not considered, though, to be good pleading. 

Com. v. Parsons, 138 Mass. 189

A forgery of a check is properly pleaded as an order for money. 

Meserve v. Com., 137 Mass. 109

A discharge of a mortgage is a deed in so far as relates to a forgery of a deed. A certificate of a justice of the peace may be received as a legal proof in charging a forgery. 

Com. v. Lawless, 101 Mass. 32 "Received of AB, Surgeon General, by discharge and check for $100" is not an accountable receipt and is bad pleading. It does not acknowledge that anything has been received which is to be accounted for.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Adams, 7 Met. 50

The endorsement of the payee is not a part of the note, merely evidence of it, and it is not necessary to set forth the manner in which the party was intended to be defrauded. 

Com. v. Buttrick, 100 Mass. 12

An averment that the whole instrument is forged is satisfied by proof of a forgery of any material part thereof. 

Com. v. White, 145 Mass. 392

A receipted bill of goods is an acquittance and discharge for money. 

Com. v. Dallinger, 118 Mass. 439

An instrument purporting to be signed by J. S. payable to the order of J. S. is not a promissory note until endorsed. Fraud. 

Com. v. Hutchinson, 114 Mass. 325

Obtaining a signature to a written instrument by means of false pretenses-form of complaint. 

Com. v. Dean, 110 Mass. 64

There must be an allegation of an intent to defraud other than the ending which alleges obtaining the same with an intent to defraud. 

Com. v. Ashton, 125 Mass. 384

Obtaining property by trick. The pleader need not allege the kind of trick, the name being unknown is sufficient. 

Com. v. Blanchette, 157 Mass. 486 

Purchasing goods under a false color-form of complaint.

Possession of goods under the above complaint will support an allegation of ownership. 

Com. v. Parker, 117 Mass. 112

A complaint which alleges that the property was obtained by trick and other means is broader than the statute and void. The complaint cannot be rejected as surplusage. 

Fugitive from justice. 

Vorhees on Arrest, page 232 

I Sandford (N. Y.), 701

The crime in a complaint for being a fugitive from justice must be set out with clearness as to the facts.

CRIMINAL CASES 

425 

Gaming. 

Com. v. Clancy, 154 Mass. 128

The pleader need not aver in a complaint for registering bets and selling pools that the defendant registered bets and sold pools. 

If he does either, he is guilty. If the business embraces more than one, it is still an offense. 

Com. v. Moody, 143 Mass. 177

The offense of occupying a room in a building with books, apparatus, etc., to sell pools, is a single averment and is not bad for duplicity. 

Com. v. Swan, 160 Mass. 354

In a complaint for occupying a room with apparatus, etc., by unknown persons, the pleader need not name them. He need name only the name of the man who is complained of for being present in the room. 

Com. v. Sheedy, 159 Mass. 55

A complaint which alleges "did dispose of one suit of clothes of the value of $55 by way of lottery" is defective. The pleader must allege to whom the suit was disposed of, and if not known, he must so plead. 

Com. v. Sullivan, 146 Mass. 142 "Did set up and promote a certain lottery for money, a more particular description of the same being to the said complainant unknown," is good. 

Com. v. Horton, 2 Gray, 69

The pleader need not allege the name of the lottery or describe the articles disposed of or state their value, or the name of the owner or of the person who received them as prizes. 

He must, however, allege that the articles were "of value." 

Com. v. Smith, 166 Mass. 370

Under a complaint for being present when gaming implements were found the pleader need not allege that the defendant had knowledge of the presence of the implements or the character of the place where they were found. 

Com. v. Warren, 161 Mass. 281

The word "place" in a search warrant means building or house, not confined to the room where such persons were found when arrested.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Ferry, 146 Mass. 203

The complaint need not allege any detailed description of the apparatus or of the particular contests. 

Hackney carriage. 

Com. v. White, 260 Mass. 300

The complaint charged "did engage in the business of transporting persons for hire." The defendant took a fare from Boston to Brookline, then took a fare from one point in Brookline to another point in Brookline. One single act is not engaging in business. Health. 

Com. v. Prince, 203 Mass. 602

Selling for food a carcass of a cow condemned as diseased is not an offense. 

Com. v. McCurdy, 5 Mass. 324

Acts for the preservation of fish are public statutes and need not be set out in an indictment. 

Com. v. Raymond, 97 Mass. 567

The state need not allege nor prove that the defendant knew that bob veal so called was under age, nor that he intended to sell it within the state. 

Com. v. Boynton, 12 Cush. 499 

An indictment for selling corrupt and diseased provisions must distinctly allege that the vendor knew that they were diseased. An allegation that he did knowingly sell is not sufficient. 

Idle and disorderly. 

Com. v. Brown, 141 Mass. 78

The pleader need not allege that the defendant was under the necessity of laboring or supporting himself or following any business or vocation; the statutory description is sufficient. 

Com. v. Diamond, 248 Mass. 511

A complaint alleging that the defendant was an idle and disorderly person is sufficient. 

Illegitimate child. 

Com. v. Baxter, 267 Mass. 591

The time and place of the begetting are not material so long as the place is within the jurisdiction of the commonwealth.

CRIMINAL CASES 

427 

Com. v. Sego, 125 Mass. 210

The pleader may allege the begetting to have taken place on "a certain day," and not "on or about," and that allegation will be supported by proof of the begetting on any day. 

Bassett v. Abbott, 4 Gray, 69

A complaint which specifies the time and the place at which the child was begotten is supported by proof that the parties had intercourse at the time and the place named, also at another time and place, and that the child was begotten by one of these acts. 

Indecent exposure. 

Com. v. Haynes, 2 Gray, 72

The present form in the Municipal Court upheld. The pleader need not conclude to the "common nuisance of all citizens," etc. 

Labor laws. 

Com. v. Dunn, 170 Mass. 140 

The pleader must allege in the complaint that certain wages are due to the employee. 

Com. v. Mullen, 176 Mass. 132

As to the department making the complaint, any person can swear to it, even if not connected with the department. 

Com. v. Dyer, 128 Mass. 70

Complaint for intimidation of employees. The pleader need not set out the means of intimidation. Form of complaint. 

Com. v. Osborne Mills, 130 Mass. 33

The pleader must use the words "did employ in laboring in a certain room." The phrase "did employ a woman in a certain room" is not sufficient; he must use the words of the statute. Larceny. 

Com. v. Gallagher, 16 Gray, 240

Need not allege the coins to be current as money within said commonwealth. 

Com. v. Pratt, 132 Mass. 246 

A complaint must allege "did steal, take, and carry away"; the omission of the word "away" is fatal.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Smith, 1 Mass. 245

In an indictment for larceny the value of the property must be alleged. 

Com. v. Pratt, 132 Mass. 246 

Where the statute sets forth the words "deemed to have committed larceny," the pleader must set forth the offense substantially and formally. 

Green v. Com., 111 Mass. 417 

A complaint alleged "feloniously did take and steal," without any averment of carrying away. The defect is one of form and not of substance. A carrying away must be proved. 

Com. v. Grimes, 10 Gray, 470

The pleader need not allege the denomination of the bills and coins; the amount and value in all is sufficient. 

Com. v. McDonald, 5 Cush. 365

It is not necessary to allege that there was anything in the pocket which could be the subject of larceny. If the pocket was empty, the offense was committed. It is the overt act. 

Com. v. Simonds, 11 Gray, 306

Where a corporation is alleged to be the owner of property the pleader must allege that it is legally incorporated by the laws of the commonwealth. 

Com. v. Bannon, 254 Mass. 320 "Did steal a sum of money of the value of more than $100 of the property of " is sufficient. .... 

Com. v. Kelley, 184 Mass. 320

A complaint alleging that the defendant did steal money the property of the estate of the late ownership. charges proper 

Com. v. Sego, 125 Mass. 210

It is not necessary to prove the time as alleged in the complaint. 

Com. v. Stebbins, 8 Gray, 492 

Com. v. Hurd, 123 Mass. 438 

Com. v. Brisbois, 281 Mass. 125 

Honest belief. See under heading of Larceny.

CRIMINAL CASES 

429 

Com. v. Greene, 111 Mass. 392 

Whiteside v. Loney, 171 Mass. 431 

Com. v. Brigham, 123 Mass. 248

An officer who attaches goods of A and subsequently A steals them from the attaching officers. The complaint can allege that the property is in the officer. 

Com. v. Cabot, 241 Mass. 131

While a thing stolen must be of some value to be the subject of larceny or the receiving of it, it need not be an article having any special value or market value. 

Com. v. Brettun, 100 Mass. 206 

One promissory note of the value of $100. One piece of paper of the value of one cent. Both can be pleaded in the same complaint, need not allege for the payment of money. “A piece of paper" is a sufficient description. 

Com. v. Weiner, 255 Mass. 506

If goods are in the possession and custody of a clerk in a store and he is attacked and robbed of them, an indictment which alleges the ownership of the goods in the true owner is good. It could have been alleged as the property either of the clerk or of the true owner. 

Com. v. Tenney, 97 Mass. 50 

Com. v. Simpson, 9 Met. 138

A statutory offense must be pleaded in the words of the statute where it reads "deemed to have committed the crime of larceny."

Larceny and receiving stolen goods. 

Com. v. Adams, 7 Gray, 43

The larceny and receiving of stolen goods may be joined in the same indictment; also the receiver and the thief may be joined. Lewdness. 

Com. v. Parker, 4 Allen, 313

Pleaded continuando. Was and still is a lewd, wanton, and lascivious person in speech and behavior. Does not set out two offenses. One instance of a single act may be sufficient.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Bradley, 2 Cush. 553

In an indictment for lewd cohabitation, if the pleader avers that A and B were married on a day certain and afterwards he married another woman and lived in this state with the last named, it is sufficient notwithstanding there is no allegation of time. 

Com. v. Dill, 159 Mass. 61

A complaint which alleges that A and B abided together instead of associated together is not defective. Libel. 

Com. v. Szliakys, 254 Mass. 424

If the words alleged to be libelous are not in themselves so, the pleader must by introduction plead, if it is new matter; by way of innuendo if it is only a matter of explanation. 

Com. v. Harmon, 2 Gray, 289

It is not necessary to set forth the whole of the paper in which the libel is contained, but that part may be selected which is most offensive. 

Com. v. Varney, 10 Cush. 402

An indictment of a libel on B need not describe B as of any profession, occupation, or place of residence. An indictment charging the defendant published a libel on the 21st of the month is supported by publication on the 19th. If the indictment alleged was published on the 21st dated, the libel would have to be proved as laid. 

Com. v. Child, 13 Pick. 198

One a member of the legislature, the pleader must aver intrinsic facts, and one otherwise pleaded is void. 

Lord's day. 

Com. v. Shannihan, 145 Mass. 99 

A complaint for keeping open a shop on the Lord's day need not negative the exceptions. 

Com. v. Nagle, 117 Mass. 142

The words "except works of necessity or charity" apply only to the clause "or does any manner of labor, business, or work," and has no reference whatever to either of the other branches of the statute.

CRIMINAL CASES 

431 

Com. v. Wright, 12 Allen, 187 

Form for keeping open shop. 

Com. v. Harrison, 11 Gray, 308

Proof need not be confined to the particular day of the month alleged; if the acts done were done on the Lord's day, that is sufficient. 

Com. v. Collins, 2 Cush. 556

On an indictment for keeping open shop there must be an allegation that it was kept open for the transaction of business, or for some other unlawful purpose. 

Com. v. Dextra, 143 Mass. 28

If the complaint alleges that the defendant "kept open his shop on the Lord's day, the same not being a work of necessity or charity," the latter phrase can be rejected as surplusage. 

Com. v. Sampson, 97 Mass. 407

Two defendants may be joined in the same complaint and may be convicted upon proof of a joint act. Lottery. 

Com. v. McKay, 177 Mass. 345

A complaint which charged "did set up and promote a lottery,” and did not allege that it was kept for money is not defective. And after a trial and conviction, cannot be taken advantage of. 

Com. v. Twitchell, 4 Cush. 74

The statute says "set up or promote." The complaint was pleaded "set up and promote." Not bad for duplicity. 

Com. v. Horton, 2 Gray, 69

In a complaint for setting up and promoting a lottery for property, the complaint must allege that the defendant did set up and promote a certain lottery for property of value. 

Com. v. Sheedy, 159 Mass. 55

A complaint which alleges "did dispose of one suit of clothes of the value of $55 by way of lottery" is void. The pleader should have alleged to whom the suit was disposed of, and if he did not know that fact, should have so pleaded.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Sullivan, 146 Mass. 142 "Did set up and promote a certain lottery for money, a more particular description of the same being to the said complainant unknown," is a good complaint. 

Malicious mischief. 

Com. v. Sullivan, 107 Mass. 218

The complaint for willfully and maliciously injuring certain goods can be maintained without an allegation that they were destroyed. 

Com. v. Williams, 110 Mass. 401

In pleading the offense of willfully and maliciously injuring a building, the pleader must allege and use the word "wantonly." 

The injury must be done out of cruelty, hostility, or revenge. 

Com. v. Falvey, 108 Mass. 304

Poisoning hens may be pleaded as willful and malicious destruction of the personal property of another. Need not allege the manner in which it was done; willfully and maliciously is sufficient. 

Com. v. Brooks, 9 Gray, 299 "Willfully" in a complaint means "intentionally," and "maliciously" imports a criminal motive, intent, or purpose. 

Com. v. Galatta, 228 Mass. 309

A complaint, under Section 127 of Chapter 266 of the Gen eral Laws, need not allege that the destruction was in a manner and by means not particularly mentioned. 

Com. v. Cox, 7 Allen, 577

The pleader need not allege the amount of damage or the value of the property destroyed. 

Com. v. Dougherty, 6 Gray, 349

A complaint for maliciously destroying a shrub on the land of another must allege an unlawful entry by the defendant on the land. Milk. 

Com. v. Luscomb, 130 Mass. 42

In a complaint charging the defendant "with having in his custody and possession with intent to sell one pint of adulterated

CRIMINAL CASES 

433 milk to which water had been added," the allegation is descriptive and is not supported by proof that the milk in question was adulterated by adding water to pure milk. 

Com. v. Tobias, 141 Mass. 129 

1st count, selling adulterated milk. 2d count, having in possession with intent to sell. This is not a merger and he could be punished on both counts; an offer to sell and actually selling are but one offense. 

Com. v. Schaffner, 146 Mass. 512

In a complaint for having in possession milk to which a foreign substance had been added, the pleader need not allege the quantity of the added matter nor the fact that it was injurious to the health; the mere adding is the offense. 

Com. v. Nichols, 10 Allen, 199 

Did unlawfully keep, offer for sale, and sell charges but one offense. Murder. 

Com. v. Osman, 284 Mass. 421

A girl was raped and then murdered. The defendant was indicted for first-degree murder. The jury found that it was done. with great atrocity. This did not make the crimes independent of each other. 

Com. v. Mercier, 257 Mass. 353

In pleading murder by the use of poison the pleader need not name the poison, and the commonwealth need not furnish the same in a bill of particulars. 

Com. v. Robertson, 162 Mass. 90

Then and there died. The date of the offense having been given, "then and there" refers to that date. The pleader need not state how the weapon was held. "In and on the head" is good pleading; need not state where on the head. 

Com. v. Snell, 189 Mass. 12 

An allegation that the deceased then and there instantly died is sufficient. Words used in the statute to define a crime or other words conveying the same meaning may be used. 

Com. v. Wakelin, 230 Mass. 567 

Simplified form of murder pleading is good. The word "kill" is not a requisite description of manslaughter.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Min Sing, 202 Mass. 121

In an indictment for first-degree murder the words "deliberately premeditated" are not necessary; a change in the form presented by Chapter 277 is sufficient. 

Com. v. Jordan, 207 Mass. 259

The pleader need not in a murder complaint set out the means by which the deceased met her death. 

Com. v. Costley, 118 Mass. 1 

In a complaint for murder by shooting with a pistol, the pleader need not allege that the pistol was held in the hands of the defendant. 

Com. v. Hersey, 2 Allen, 173

In an indictment for killing by poison it is not necessary to allege that the poison was administered with intent to kill. 

Com. v. Stafford, 12 Cush. 619

An indictment for murder cannot be quashed because it alleges that the death was caused by a series of beatings administered on different days and months of the year. 

Com. v. Fox, 7 Gray, 585

Acts which hasten the death of a person already suffering from a disease of which she must probably have soon died, may be laid, in an indictment for murder, as the sole cause of death, without mentioning the disease. 

Com. v. Chapman, 11 Cush. 422

If the defendant is present aiding and abetting a felony so as to make him a principal, in the second degree, that evidence would support an indictment charging him as the sole principal, as having struck the blow with his own hand. 

Neglect of family. 

Com. v. Burlington, 136 Mass. 435

Did unreasonably neglect and refuse to provide for the support and maintenance of said wife, she the said wife being then and on said other days and times the lawful wife of him the said defendant. Plead continuando.

Nuisances. 

Com. v. Quinlan, 153 Mass. 483

In a complaint for a nuisance the place need not be particularly set forth.

CRIMINAL CASES 

435 

Com. v. Bartley, 138 Mass. 181

Must allege in the complaint the name of the person using the premises. 

Com. v. Bossidy, 112 Mass. 277

If the defendant let the whole of the building to the tenant and he used only a part of it, the complaint is good; but if the tenant only occupied a tenement in the building, it must so be pleaded. 

Com. v. Lapointe, 228 Mass. 266

A complaint for permitting a building to be used for the purposes of prostitution cannot be maintained on the evidence of letting. 

Com. v. Stahl, 7 Allen, 303

In an indictment for a nuisance the words "resorted to" must be alleged. A man might use his premises for illegal gaming and not violate the law, but if he allows persons to resort there, he does. The pleader must allege that he kept, etc., for the purpose of unlawful gaming and permitted persons to frequent there. 

Com. v. Donovan, 16 Gray, 18

Did keep and maintain a certain tenement, to wit, a room in the dwelling house used as a house of ill fame, gaming, and liquor, whereby it is a nuisance. 

Com. v. Rumford Chem. Co., 16 Gray, 231

Form of complaint for odors emanating from the manufacture of chemicals. 

Com. v. Howe, 13 Gray, 26

An indictment need not conclude "to the common nuisance of all citizens." 

Com. v. Skelly, 10 Gray, 464 "Keeping a certain tenement in Portland Street," etc., is sufficient without a more particular description. 

Com. v. Moore, 11 Cush. 600

Letting premises for prostitution is not a continuous offense, and a day certain must be alleged. Also the name of the lessee and that the lease was received and accepted by the individual to whom it was made. 

Com. v. Lee, 148 Mass. 8

A building wholly occupied by the defendant and used by him in whole or in part may be described as a building, or tenement,

CRIMINAL CASES 

but a tenement is not necessarily an entire building, it may be a part of a building, and proof that the defendant occupied a part and used it illegally, while other persons occupied other parts, will not support a charge of keeping and maintaining a building. 

Com. v. McCauhey, 9 Gray, 296

Keeping an apartment in a building, which apartment is held separately from the rest of the building, is not keeping and maintaining a building, but is the keeping and maintaining of a tenement within the meaning of the statute. 

Obscenity. 

Com. v. Wright, 139 Mass. 382

In setting out the complaint, the obscene picture or thing must at least by some general description identify the paper. 

The pleader need not set out the same in toto, but may allege that it is too obscene to be more fully described. 

Com. v. Holmes, 17 Mass. 336

In an indictment for publishing an obscene book it is sufficient to give a general description and to allege their evil tendency, without copying the book or minutely describing the print. 

Com. v. McCance, 164 Mass. 162

As to the description of obscene parts in a book. 

Com. v. Allison, 227 Mass. 57

A description of an obscene pamphlet without enumerating any description is good; a bill of particulars is the remedy. 

Com. v. Dejardin, 126 Mass. 46

An allegation that the defendant printed and published pictures of naked girls is not sustained by proof that he published obscene pictures of girls naked above the waist only. 

Officer, assuming to be an. 

Com. v. Wolcott, 10 Cush. 61

The pleader must allege that he falsely assumed and pretended to be an officer and took upon himself to act as a sheriff of the commonwealth. 

Obstructing justice. 

Com. v. Reynolds, 14 Gray, 87

If the indictment is at common law and the pleader ends it with words contrary to the form of the statute, they may be

CRIMINAL CASES 

437 

rejected as surplusage. See Chapter 277, Section 33, of the Gen eral Laws. 

Park commissioners. 

Com. v. Butler, 204 Mass. 11

A public way under the jurisdiction of the park commissioners is still a public way and may be alleged as such; the commissioners only exercise jurisdiction over it.

Perjury. 

Com. v. Johnson, 175 Mass. 152

The indictment need not set forth the facts that at the time of asking the witness questions a copy of the conviction of the witness was in the hands of the inquirer. 

Com. v. Bouvier, 164 Mass. 398

The pleader need not allege that the court was held for criminal business, if the allegation was before a district court. 

Com. v. Knight, 12 Mass. 274

The complaint must allege that the question is a material one. Need not allege that the witness was subpoenaed nor that he appeared voluntarily. 

Com. v. Terry, 114 Mass. 263 

Form of complaint for falsely answering interrogatories. 

Com. v. Devine, 155 Mass. 224

Perjury and subornation of perjury can be joined in the same complaint. 

Com. v. McCarthy, 152 Mass. 571

The substantive matter may be shown to be material by a direct allegation or by allegations from which its materiality appears. 

Com. v. Stone, 152 Mass. 571

In an indictment for subornation of perjury it is not necessary to allege the residence of the person whose testimony was suborned, but such residence if alleged must be proved as laid. 

Com. v. Knight, 12 Mass. 274

It is sufficient that the perjury was committed in an issue duly joined without an express allegation that the cause of action was within the jurisdiction of the court. Must allege that facts representing were in answer to a material fact.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Aronson, 250 Mass. 520 

Com. v. Carel, 105 Mass. 582

Surety offering false title to property. Form of complaint for. 

Com. v. Hughes, 5 Allen, 499

The complaint need not allege the authority of the person before whom the perjury was committed. 

Com. v. Byron, 14 Gray, 31

The complaint must allege that the question on which the perjury is based is a material one. Polygamy. 

Com. v. McGrath, 140 Mass. 296

The allegation of time of the first marriage is not material and need not be proved as laid. 

Com. v. Putnam, 1 Pick. 136

After a divorce for the adultery of the husband, he does not, by marrying and cohabitating with the second wife, commit the crime of adultery, but ought to be indicted for polygamy, and the second marriage, with all the other facts constituting his offense, should be set forth in the indictment. 

Com. v. Bradley, 2 Cush. 553

In an indictment for continuing to cohabit in this state with a second wife, the defendant having a former wife living, it is sufficient statement of the time when the offense was committed, to allege that the second marriage was on a certain day, and that the defendant "afterwards did cohabit and continue to cohabit with the said A in said county for a long space of time, to wit, for the space of five months." The complaint could allege "from time to time did cohabit and continue to cohabit." 

Com. v. Hunt, 4 Cush. 49

Where a husband obtained a divorce in this state and his wife went into another state and there contracted a marriage to avoid the law in this state, and then she returned to this state with her husband, she was guilty of polygamy here, not lewd and lascivious cohabitation. 

Com. v. Jennings, 121 Mass. 47 

The exceptions in the polygamy statute need not be negatived.

Form for living in this state after being married elsewhere.
