439 

Poor debtor. 

Com. v. Martin, 130 Mass. 465

Form and procedure for a poor debtor who conceals the assets of his estate. 

Railer and brawler. 

Stratton v. Com., 10 Met. 217

The complaint need not set out the specific acts of railing and brawling. Rape. 

Com. v. Sugland, 4 Gray, 7

A complaint which charges rape is sufficient without charging and stating the age of the female. If the complaint charges "against her will," the offense is for a female over the age of consent. 

Com. v. Scannell, 11 Cush. 547

The pleader need not allege that the ravished woman was not the wife of the defendant. 

Receiving stolen goods. 

Com. v. Hogan, 121 Mass. 373 

Com. v. Slate, 11 Gray, 60 

Com. v. King, 9 Cush. 284 

The complaint need not allege the name of the thief, but if alleged, that fact must be proved as laid. 

Com. v. Lakeman, 5 Gray, 82

The complaint need not allege that the goods were taken and carried away, it does not have to allege with the same degree of certainty as a complaint for larceny. 

O'Connell v. Com., 7 Met. 460

A complaint for receiving stolen goods which alleges divers articles and their value is good, but the defendant's plea of guilty to receiving fifty dollars' worth of the said property is a void plea and no judgment can be rendered on such a plea. 

Com. v. Harris, 232 Mass. 588

A complaint for conspiracy to receive stolen goods, alleging the crimes to be committed as time occurs in the future, is good pleading.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Riot. 

Com. v. Gibney, 2 Allen, 150

A complaint for riot must charge an unlawful assembling together by the defendants. 

Com. v. Runnells, 10 Mass. 518

It is sufficient to allege that the defendants assembled with force and arms and being so assembled did, etc., without repeating the phrase "force and arms." 

Com. v. Frishman, 235 Mass. 449

Description and form of a complaint of a riot at common law. Robbery. 

Com. v. Gallagher, 6 Met. 565

A complaint which alleges that the defendant assaulted and robbed A, and being armed with a dangerous weapon did strike and wound A, is not proved by evidence of a slight scratch on the face of A, nor as to the striking by evidence that the defendant put his arms around the neck of A and threw him to the ground. 

Com. v. Cody, 165 Mass. 133

Form of complaint when the defendant is armed. 

Com. v. Clifford, 8 Cush. 215

The complaint must allege that the property taken was the property of the person robbed or of a third person, and that person was put in fear. If the pleader used the phrase "by force and violence," the allegation of putting in fear is unnecessary. 

Com. v. Weiner, 255 Mass. 506

If goods are in the possession of a clerk in a store and he is attacked and robbed, the complaint may allege the goods as his property or the property of the true owner. 

Com. v. Mowry, 11 Allen, 20

In an indictment for robbery while armed with a dangerous weapon and wounding and striking the person robbed, it is not necessary to allege the wounding and striking was done with the dangerous weapon. 

Railroad laws. 

Com. v. Carroll, 145 Mass. 403

Throwing missiles at a railroad car. It does not make any difference whether the car was in use or not at the time of throwing.

CRIMINAL CASES 

441 

Com. v. McCarthy, 145 Mass. 575 

An allegation that the defendant threw a missile, to wit, a stone, is not sustained by proof that he threw a billet of wood. 

Com. v. Pope, 12 Cush. 272

In a complaint for obstructing a railroad alleged to be the Boston & Worcester Railroad Company, the corporation was the Boston & Worcester Railroad Corporation. Indictment was void. Sodomy. 

Com. v. Dill, 160 Mass. 536

The complaint alleged "unlawfully and feloniously did commit a certain unnatural and lascivious act with one 

"Did commit the abominable and detestable crime against nature with one is better pleading. 

PLUMBER GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 142 

Com. v. Beulieu, 213 Mass. 138

Acts of 1909, Chapter 536, Section 4, doing plumbing sub ject to inspection without being licensed or registered, is a journeyman plumber. 

Com. v. McCarthy, 225 Mass. 192

The state has the right to pass laws as to health regulations. 

A complaint for engaging in the business of a master plumber without a license. A journeyman has the right to work for himself and take contracts for work to do by his own labor, but has not the right to employ others as journeyman plumbers to assist him, as that would make him a master plumber.

POLICE OFFICER 

Right to arrest. See under Arrest. 

Hathaway v. City of Everett, 205 Mass. 246

The city is not liable to persons assaulted and robbed by a police officer.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Sawyer, 142 Mass. 530

A police officer has all the powers of a constable except the power of civil process. General knowledge of persons assaulting police officers to show their knowledge that he was an officer; living in the neighborhood and seeing him there is sufficient. 

Com. v. Ducey, 126 Mass. 269

Police officers may inspect licensed premises for the purpose of observing how the business is carried on, but this does not authorize any search or seizure of persons or property. The superior officer need not specially designate any particular place. 

Healey v. Lothrop, 171 Mass. 263 Healey v. Lothrop, 178 Mass. 151 

A special officer is alone liable on his bond and does not make him the servant of the person at whose request he was appointed. 

The suit must be against the bond given by the employer to the 

City Treasurer. 

Com. v. Kane, 108 Mass. 423

One who is acting as a public officer and has been duly appointed to the office which he assumes to exercise; all his acts done by what appears to be public authority are presumed to be rightly done until the contrary is proved. If officer has been acting as such for the term of four years, that is sufficient to prove that he is an officer. 

Com. v. Cushing, 99 Mass. 592 

Com. v. Dugan, 12 Met. 233

The appointment of a man on police duty constitutes him a police officer without his being sworn in. General Laws, Chap ter 18, Section 38, of General Laws. 

Com. v. Hastings, 9 Met. 259

A special officer's duty is not confined to the walls of the theater, but extends to the environs to keep the peace and protect the patrons, to shops, stalls and stands kept in the vicinity supplying refreshments to the theater. 

Arrest without warrant. 

Rohan v. Sawin, 5 Cush. 281

A person may be lawfully arrested without a warrant upon reasonable grounds to believe that such person has committed a felony.

CRIMINAL CASES 

443 

Com. v. Hastings, 9 Met. 259

A constable as a conservator of the peace has power to arrest, upon view, persons violating the law, and detain them until they can be brought before a magistrate. 

Rohan v. Sawin, 5 Cush. 281

He may arrest having reasonable grounds to suspect one of receiving or aiding in the concealment of stolen goods, knowing them to be stolen; without a warrant arrest the supposed offender. 

Mason v. Lothrop, 7 Gray, 354

An officer cannot arrest one without a warrant on mere probable cause for believing that he is engaged in unlawfully transporting liquor. 

Com. v. Presby, 14 Gray, 65

An officer may without a warrant take a drunk into custody, although such person may not be so in fact. 

Com. v. O'Connor, 7 Allen, 583

An officer cannot, except as expressly authorized by statute, arrest for the crime of drunkenness without a warrant. 

Leddy v. Crossman, 108 Mass. 237

An officer may without a warrant arrest one who violently resists the execution of a lawful search warrant. 

Com. v. Tobin, 108 Mass. 426

A peace officer may without a warrant arrest for breaches of the peace committed in his presence. 

Krulevitz v. Eastern Ry., 143 Mass. 228

Officers cannot without a warrant arrest a passenger for refusal to pay a fare upon complaint of the conductor, where such refusal is not made in their presence. 

Ford v. Breen, 173 Mass. 52

May arrest one for drunkenness in his own dwelling house. 

Authority under warrant. 

Com. v. Field, 13 Mass. 321

The right to demand one, not generally known as an officer, to produce his warrant is when the party submits himself to the arrest, not when he immediately resists.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Cooley, 6 Gray, 350

An officer making an arrest by virtue of a warrant is not bound to exhibit his warrant and read it to the prisoner before securing him, if he resists. 

Com. v. Irwine, 1 Allen, 587

An officer who has obtained entrance to the house of a third person for the purpose of searching for one against whom he holds a warrant on a criminal charge, is not bound to exhibit his warrant to the householder, provided the latter has reasonable notice that he is an officer, and acting under a warrant against a person supposed to be there. 

Mode of making arrest. 

Com. v. Field, 13 Mass. 321

When one, not generally known as an officer, makes an arrest, he must show his authority, if demanded. 

Bernard v. Bartlett, 10 Cush. 501

An officer acting bona fide may break and enter another's dwelling house to arrest him on a criminal charge, although in the mistaken belief that such person is in the house at the time, provided he first requested an entrance, and be guilty of no unnecessary damage or violence. 

Jacob v. Measures, 13 Gray, 74

An officer in the execution of valid criminal process is justified in breaking the outer door of a dwelling house after demanding admittance and being refused. 

Ford v. Breen, 173 Mass. 52

Police officers finding open the door of a dwelling house in which an intoxicated person is commiting a breach of the peace have a right to enter, without an express or implied invitation. 

Custody and disposition of prisoner. 

Tubbs v. Tukey, 3 Cush. 438

Where an officer on Sunday arrests an accused under a warrant which commands the body of the accused be brought before the police court, the officer cannot, under the warrant, justify his acts in allowing the accused to remain in jail longer than Monday morning.

CRIMINAL CASES 

445 

Rohan v. Sawin, 5 Cush. 281

A peace officer having lawfully arrested one without a warrant may detain him for a reasonable time for the purpose of securing him to answer to a complaint. 

Com. v. Morihan, 4 Allen, 585

An officer's custody of a prisoner whom he has arrested upon a warrant, and brought before a court for trial, does not cease until the prisoner has been discharged, or a warrant of commitment made out. 

Brock v. Stimson, 108 Mass. 520

An officer arresting one without a warrant for intoxication in a public place must take him before a magistrate. 

Keefe v. Hart, 213 Mass. 476 

Wax v. McGrath, 255 Mass. 340

An officer who arrests a man without a warrant must take him before the court, and the court is to decide whether there is enough evidence to grant process. He cannot be held as a suspicious person, he must be charged with a crime. 

Property of the prisoner. 

King v. Ham, 6 Allen, 298

Property taken from a prisoner which is not in any way connected with the offense charged, should be surrendered on demand, after recognizance has been given. 

Second arrest. 

Com. v. Hastings, 9 Met. 259

An officer who arrests one for disorderly conduct and releases him on the promise that he will go directly home, may lawfully retake him into custody if he sees him going into a bar room before he is out of the officer's sight. 

Com. v. MaGahey, 11 Gray, 194

A constable endeavoring to rearrest a prisoner who has escaped, after demand and refusal of admission, may break doors. 

Police power. 

Com. v. Maletsky, 203 Mass. 241

Where the legislature grants unlimited power to certain designated bodies or officers, an ordinance by which they delegate this power absolutely to a subordinate is void.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Slocum, 230 Mass. 180

Police powers extend to reasonable control of travel upon highways in the interests of the public; includes automobiles. 

The legislature may delegate authority to enact laws. 

Pleading. 

POLYGAMY GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 272, SEC. 15 

Com. v. McGrath, 140 Mass. 296

The allegation of the time of the first marriage is not material and need not be proved as laid. 

Com. v. Putnam, 1 Pick. 136

After a divorce for the adultery of her husband, he does not, by marrying and cohabitating with the second wife, commit the crime of adultery, but ought to be indicted for polygamy, and the second marriage, with all the other facts constituting the offense, should be set forth in the indictment. 

Com. v. Bradley, 2 Cush. 553

In an indictment for continuing to cohabit in this state with a second wife, the defendant having a former wife living, it is a sufficient statement of the time when the offense was committed, to allege that the second marriage was on a day certain and that the defendant "afterwards did cohabit and continue to cohabit with the said A in said country for a long space of time, to wit, for the space of five months." The complaint could allege "from time to time did cohabit and continue to cohabit." 

Com. v. Hunt, 4 Cush. 49

Where a husband obtained a divorce in this state and his wife went into another state and there contracted a marriage to evade the law in this state, and then returned to this state with her second husband, she was guilty of polygamy here and not guilty of lewd and lascivious cohabitation. 

Com. v. Jennings, 121 Mass. 47 

The exceptions in the polygamy statute need not be negatived.

Form of complaint for living in this state after marrying elsewhere.

CRIMINAL CASES 

447 

Com. v. Godsoe, 105 Mass. 464

An indictment alleging that the second marriage occurred on a certain day (December 1, 1868) and the defendant afterwards cohabited in this state for a period of six months, sufficiently describes the offense. This may be sustained by evidence of his cohabitating with her at any time during that period; that is, evidence could be introduced between December 1 and June 1, 1869. 

Evidence. 

Com. v. Johnson, 10 Allen, 196

In order to prove a former marriage in England a witness may testify to the general repute there of the marriage. A man who left his wife in England and married in the United States thirteen years later, cannot defend on the seven years' presumption. 

Com. v. Boyer, 7 Allen, 306

If the defendant in an indictment for polygamy relies on a divorce as a justification of the second marriage, it is incumbent upon him to prove it; it does not follow the issue of proof. The defendant can with perfect ease show that the woman was legally divorced, while the state could not, without great difficulty, show the negative. Where the defendant in the first instance is shown to have done an act which was unlawful, unless he was distinctly authorized to do it, the proof of the authority is thrown on him. 

Com. v. Mash, 7 Met. 472

A husband voluntarily left his wife. Four years later he married again. An honest belief that she was dead was not any excuse. Guilty. 

Com. v. Stevens, 196 Mass. 280

Chapter 207 of the General Laws does not apply to persons living together as man and wife in good faith, who lived in another state. It only applies in Massachusetts to people living here when the impediment was removed. 

Com. v. Josselyn, 186 Mass. 186

The defendant married the second wife within two years of the divorce and they lived together as man and wife. The defendant married a third time. Guilty of polygamy. Chapter 207, Section 6, of the General Laws. 

Com. v. Hayden, 163 Mass. 453

An honest and reasonable belief in the death of a former husband or wife is not in this state a defense to a prosecution for polygamy.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Kenney, 120 Mass. 387

Parties married in Ireland. Came to Canada. He left her there and came here and married in Harwich. He left Harwich and returned to the first wife, cohabitating with her in Boston on the way back to Canada. Jurisdiction is in Harwich. 

Com. v. Ross, 248 Mass. 15

The defendant was indicted for polygamy, which alleged that he lived with the second wife six years preceding the finding of the indictment. Evidence of the illegal cohabitation could be introduced prior to the six years, and the statute of limitations did not affect the same. 

Com. v. Lucas, 158 Mass. 81

To continue to cohabit with such second husband or wife means to dwell together as husband and wife ordinarily do, but does not imply sexual intercourse. Must actually cohabit in this state to take jurisdiction, otherwise the second marriage, being consummated in Maine, the jurisdiction would be there. 

Com. v. Lee, 143 Mass. 100

The defendant went to New York for a few days with a woman. No license was produced, there was no record in New 

York. The woman testified that she wrote to her mother that she had been married in New York. They lived as man and wife for a period of six months. No other evidence of the first marriage. Not guilty. 

Com. v. Lane, 113 Mass. 458

A man who was lawfully married in this state and whose wife divorced him on the grounds of adultery, without leave of court and still being a resident of this state, is married to another woman in another state according to its laws. He afterwards cohabits with her in this state, the first wife being still alive. He is not guilty of polygamy, without proof that the second wife was a resident of this state and that he and she went into another state to evade the performance of the statute. 

Com. v. Boyer, 7 Allen, 306

If the defendant in an indictment for polygamy relies on a divorce as a justification of a second marriage, it is incumbent on him to prove it.

CRIMINAL CASES 

449 

Com. v. Thompson, 6 Allen, 591

A man who in good faith marries and cohabits with a woman whose husband has remained absent for more than seven years together without being heard from, and is believed by both parties to be dead, cannot be convicted of the crime of adultery therefor, although in fact her husband is still living. 

Com. v. Thompson, 11 Allen, 23

A man may be convicted of adultery who in good faith and in the belief that she is a widow marries and cohabits with a woman who has left her husband and remained absent from him for more than seven years together without hearing from him, if in fact her husband is still living. 

Com. v. Richardson, 126 Mass. 34

A complaint for polygamy alleged that did unlawfully marry and take for his wife one A, the said defendant then having a wife living and never having been legally divorced therefrom. 

The evidence showed that she (the first wife) had obtained a divorce, hence the defendant should have been acquitted. 

Whippen v. Whippen, 147 Mass. 294

If a husband who has been deserted by his wife for seven years, and who has no actual knowledge that she is alive, goes through a form of marriage and cohabits with another woman, he is guilty of adultery, and is precluded from obtaining a divorce for such desertion, there being no presumption of the wife's death, and it not appearing that he had no belief or reason to believe that she was alive, or information which would lead upon inquiry to actual knowledge. 

POSTMASTER 

Keenan v. Southworth, 110 Mass. 474

A postmaster is a public officer and he is not responsible for the loss of a letter occasioned by the negligence of his clerk, although he selected him, and the clerk is subject to his orders. 

Amending the complaint.

PRACTICE 

Com. v. Cooper, 264 Mass. 378

In an indictment for arson the district attorney amended, against the objections of the defendant, by adding the words

CRIMINAL CASES 

"maliciously and willfully." As these words go to the root of the indictment and change it from the one that the defendant was indicted on, the court was in error in permitting the same to be done. 

Com. v. Friede, 271 Mass. 318

A complaint subject to the defendant's objections may be amended in the Superior Court. 

Com. v. Snow, 269 Mass. 598

An indictment cannot be amended as to the matter of sub stance, and Section 35A of Chapter 277 of the General Laws per mits this to be done only so far as the defense of the defendant is not prejudiced. Amended as to matters of form only. 

Com. v. Gedzium, 259 Mass. 453 Changing the name of the defendant, that is, permitting the real name of the defendant to be entered on the indictment, is not an amendment. The defendant was indicted under the name 

John Doe. A mistake in the name of the defendant is ground only for a plea in abatement, and failure to make the same admits the sufficiency of the description. 

Defects and errors in the complaint. 

Com. v. Welsh, 174 Mass. 327

An error in the court record or in the recognizance is no ground for an arrest of judgment. 

Flito's Case, 210 Mass. 33

Whether the complaint is void, not being taken under oath, the defendant was sentenced under a mittimus, he cannot take habeas corpus proceedings, exceptions being the only method.

Sentenced in the Superior Court on appeal. 

Com. v. Galetta, 228 Mass. 308

A defect in a complaint cannot be taken advantage of by an arrest of judgment. A motion to dismiss the complaint in the 

Superior Court is too late, for a defect in a complaint which originated in the lower court. 

Com. v. Soderquist, 183 Mass. 199

The courts have the right to correct mistakes in their records. 

An arraignment is not a trial. During the sitting of the court it can vacate orders.

CRIMINAL CASES 

451 

Follins v. Dill, 229 Mass. 321

A trial court has the power to vacate an order and amend the docket in conformity to previous orders. 

Com. v. Fletcher, 157 Mass. 14

A motion to dismiss a complaint in a criminal case which is based on facts not appearing on the record cannot be sustained, although the truth of such facts is admitted by the state. 

Murphy v. Com., 172 Mass. 264 

Walsh v. Com., 224 Mass. 39

An error apparent on the record may be corrected by a writ of error. 

Com. v. Conlin, 184 Mass. 195

A judgment in the Superior Court cited "brought before the court by virtue of a warrant issued," etc. The court will assume that the warrant was duly issued and served. 

Com. v. Brown, 150 Mass. 334

A motion in arrest of judgment can be sustained only for errors apparent on the record. 

Barringer v. Northridge, 266 Mass. 315

The Superior Court has the power to correct clerical errors in its record and decrees. 

Lebowich v. Com., 235 Mass. 357

An objection to a complaint for irregularities must be taken advantage of by a plea in abatement. 

Com. v. Holmes, 119 Mass. 195

Irregularities in the proceedings upon a complaint in a district court are no grounds for dismissing the complaint in the Superior 

Court on an appeal. 

Com. v. Chiovaro, 129 Mass. 489

An objection to a complaint for a formal defect apparent on the face thereof, which does not affect the jurisdiction of the court, is not open upon a motion in arrest of judgment after a plea of guilty. 

Mistake in the name of the defendant. 

Com. v. Gedzium, 259 Mass. 453

A mistake or error in the name of the defendant in an indictment is not necessarily fatal to its validity.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Fredericks, 119 Mass. 199

The misnomer in a complaint of the defendant which is not apparent on the record cannot be availed of by a motion to dismiss but only by a plea in abatement. 

Signing a complaint. 

Com. v. Sullivan, 14 Gray, 97

A complainant may sign a complaint by a mark without any attesting witness.

Continuances. 

Com. v. Festo, 251 Mass. 275

As to the granting of a continuance when an attorney is engaged in one case to try another, it is within the discretion of the court. 

Com. v. Bannon, 97 Mass. 214

During the continuance of a term in the Superior Court there is no rule which requires daily adjournment. 

Com. v. Maloney, 145 Mass. 205

A trial justice cannot continue a trial nisi, the defendant was brought into court, pleaded guilty, and paid costs and was continued nisi. Subsequently he was notified to come into court, was sentenced and appealed. Continuances must be to a day certain except in the Superior Court, when they can be continued from term to term. 

Com. v. Friedman, 256 Mass. 214

The fact that the attorney had just entered the case is not grounds for a continuance. It is a matter wholly within the discretion of the court. The fact that a newspaper is prejudicial to the defendant affords no grounds for a continuance. 

Reopening of the case. 

Com. v. Coyne, 228 Mass. 269 

Brittian v. West End Ry., 168 Mass. 10

It is within the province of the trial justice to allow the state to reopen its case and introduce evidence. 

Com. v. Brown, 130 Mass. 279 

Com. v. Meaney, 151 Mass. 55

Allowing the prosecution in a criminal case after it has closed to reopen its case and introduce additional evidence is within the soundest discretion of the trial court.

CRIMINAL CASES 

453 

Com. v. Ricketson, 5 Met. 412

Where a jury, after a cause is committed to them and they have gone out, return and make inquiry of the court as to a fact, it is within the discretionary power of the court to admit testimony respecting the matter of such inquiries. 

Com. v. Eastman, 1 Cush. 189

It is competent for a party after having closed its case so far as relates to the evidence, to introduce additional evidence by the cross-examination of the witnesses on the other side, for the purpose of more fully proving his case. 

Com. v. Moulton, 4 Gray, 39 

Com. v. Arrance, 5 Allen, 517 

Com. v. Blair, 126 Mass. 40

No exception lies to the admission of additional testimony in behalf of the state after the defendant in the indictment has closed his case. 

Com. v. Dower, 4 Allen, 297

No exception lies to the decision of the Supreme Court in a capital case excluding additional evidence offered in defense, after the closing argument for the prisoner has been finished, and therefore the state has been begun. 

Com. v. McGorty, 114 Mass. 299

It is within the discretion of the court in a criminal case to permit a government witness to be called during the defense. 

Com. v. Hogan, 249 Mass. 555

The court may reopen a case after the state has rested. 

Com. v. Coyne, 228 Mass. 269

The order of evidence is within the discretionary power of the presiding justice, who, in the exercise of such discretion, after the defendant has introduced some evidence, may permit the district attorney to reopen the case and introduce further evidence. 

Defendant waiving form and substance. 

Com. v. Dailey, 12 Cush. 80

A defendant may waive any matter of form and substance excepting only what may relate to the jurisdiction of the court. 

Place of the trial. 

Com. v. Handren, 261 Mass. 294

After a jury had been impaneled the court adjourned with the defendant, attorney, clerk, and jury to the house of a sick wit-

CRIMINAL CASES 

ness and heard his testimony against the objections of the defendant. Verdict was set aside. 

Service of precepts. 

Com. v. Boon, 2 Gray, 74

The omission in the warrant of the command "to make return of the same and of the doings thereon" affords no ground for the discharge of the defendant. 

Jones v. Walker, 15 Gray, 353 "Last and usual place of abode" signed by an officer on a service is sufficient even if in fact it is not so. 

Com. v. Cummings, 16 Mass. 194

A notice left at one's shop is not a good service. The last and usual place of abode is construed to mean a dwelling house. 

Com. v. Colligan, 158 Mass. 163

The officer testifying was asked if the warrant on which the arrest was made was in his possession, and he replied that it was in the possession of the lower court; not required to produce it. 

Com. v. Ferden, 141 Mass. 28

The mere fact that the warrant is not in proper form does not warrant quashing the complaint. 

Com. v. Ward, 4 Mass. 497

The mittimus must recite the complaint on which it is founded. 

When is the defendant before the court? 

Com. v. Gregory, 7 Gray, 498

When the defendant appears and pleads, he is fully before the court, and the court has jurisdiction of the case. A motion to dismiss for want of due service must be made before a general appearance in the action.

Defendant in court, arrested without a warrant, no warrant issued thereafter. 

Com. v. Gorman, 288 Mass. 294

The fact that the officer did not make a return on the complaint under Chapter 218, Section 34, of the General Laws does not entitle the defendant to any rights. In this case the defendant was arrested without a warrant for operating an automo-

CRIMINAL CASES 

455 bile under the influence of liquor and no warrant was afterwards issued. He gave bail for his appearance before the court. The fact that the defendant was physically before the court is enough and he can be tried; the return is not essential. 

Effect of a dismissal. 

Com. v. Mitchell, 258 Mass. 89 

Bannon v. Auger, 262 Mass. 427 

Com. v. Bressant, 126 Mass. 246

An order of the court dismissing a complaint is not a finding that the defendant is guilty or not guilty. The fact that some evidence was heard makes no difference. If the dismissal was without the consent of the defendant, after witnesses were heard a plea of former acquittal would lie. Such an entry is like a nol pros without the consent of the defendant. 

Jurisdiction declined. 

Com. v. Rice, 216 Mass. 480

The lower court can decline jurisdiction even after a finding of guilty; the court can recall and even sentence after it has ordered the defendant to recognize.

Trial new trial. 

Com. v. Marrelli, 266 Mass. 113

Within one year after a sentence a prisoner who is committed can make a motion for a new trial. 

Com. v. Costello, 121 Mass. 371

In a motion for a new trial the defendant being in custody has no right to be present. If he is at liberty, a motion will not be heard unless he is present on the theory that if the motion is denied the court wants him where it can lay its hands on him. 

Com. v. Scott, 123 Mass. 418

In a case tried in the Superior Court and taken to the Supreme Court on exceptions, then overruled and returned to the Superior Court, a motion for a new trial in grounds not affecting the correctness of the decision must be made in the court then having the jurisdiction, to wit, the Superior Court. 

Com. v. Morrison, 134 Mass. 189

A motion for a new trial based on something which occurred since the trial, or which was not known until after the trial, which

CRIMINAL CASES 

raises questions of law, to the rulings, upon which exceptions will lie, is governed by statute. 

Com. v. Sacco, 259 Mass. 128

The granting or the denial of a motion for a new trial rests in the judicial discretion of the trial justice. 

Walsh v. Com., 224 Mass. 39

The Supreme Court may or may not remand the defendant to the Superior Court for a new trial and may assess the costs against the state. 

Com. v. Phelan, 271 Mass. 21

A motion to vacate judgment and withdraw a plea of guilty not filed before the end of the sitting at which the sentence was imposed is too late as a motion to modify the sentence. 

Com. v. Snyder, 284 Mass. 367

A motion for a new trial in capital cases comes too late to be made after sentence has been pronounced and more than a year after the verdict. The year is to be computed from the end of the trial and not from the end of the sitting ending in which the trial took place. 

Com. v. Roby, 12 Pick. 496

A new trial was granted because the judge, after the court had adjourned, wrote a letter to the jury respecting the cause which had been committed to them. Conduct of the jury during the trial is the basis of a motion. Cider may be furnished to the jurors but not spirituous or intoxicating liquor. 

Conduct of trial. 

Com. v. Leach, 156 Mass. 99

Several substantive crimes may be charged in the same complaint, where the mode of the trial and the nature of the punishment are the same. 

Com. v. Bingham, 158 Mass. 169

Persons jointly indicted to be tried together. The fact that admissions made by one defendant are not in evidence against the other is not grounds for a separate trial. 

Com. v. King, 202 Mass. 379

A general finding may be returned on each count.

CRIMINAL CASES 

457 

Com. v. Borasky, 214 Mass. 313 

Where two defendants are indicted jointly, whether they shall be tried separately is within the discretion of the court. 

Com. v. Seeley, 167 Mass. 163

Two persons indicted jointly for adultery can be tried together. 

Com. v. Rosenthal, 211 Mass. 50

A defendant against his objection may be tried on two indictments at the same time. The defendant was charged with abduction and adultery. 

One complaint for a nuisance, and one for exposing and keeping for sale intoxicating liquors, can be tried together, overruling Com. v. Bickum, 153 Mass. 386. 

Com. v. Lane, 157 Mass. 462

If the defendant relies on a formal defect in the complaint, he must point it out, and if he goes to trial without having done so, the objection is no longer open to him. 

Com. v. Lawless, 258 Mass. 262 

In a misdemeanor, agreed that eleven jurors can try him. 

Com. v. Kemp, 254 Mass. 190

Under Section 6 of Chapter 263 of the General Laws the de fendant in the Superior Court can, except in a capital case, waive his right to a jury trial, and be tried before a single justice.

In the above case this only applies to misdemeanors, but the statutes have changed this since. Now applies to felonies. 

Com. v. Holmes, 119 Mass. 195

Where two counts in one indictment charge two crimes precisely alike, the fault is cured by the election of the prosecutor to proceed upon one count only, and his entering a nol pros as to the other. 

Com. v. Coyne, 207 Mass. 21

The defendant was charged with the unlawful sale of liquor to a minor; the state introduced evidence of two distinct sales on different days. The defendant had the right to require the state to elect which sale they were to rely on for a conviction.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Herman, 253 Mass. 516

The entire control of the conduct in a criminal case is in the hands of the district attorney. An attorney who has represented the state can sit in the bar enclosure and the district attorney can consult with him, as he, the district attorney, can seek aid and assistance from any source. 

Com. v. Gedzium, 259 Mass. 453

Where one justice has heard a case and has been taken ill, another justice can hear motions for a new trial. 

Com. v. Adams, 127 Mass. 15

A misjoinder of counts is cured by a verdict of acquittal upon one of the counts. 

Com. v. Snyder, 282 Mass. 401

The defendant is not entitled to accompany the jury when they view the premises. 

Com. v. Davis, 271 Mass. 99

The extent of the punishment which may be imposed is not a factor which the jury may take into consideration in determining the guilt or innocence of the defendant. 

Com. v. Phelan, 271 Mass. 21

A motion for a new trial does not lie after a sentence following a plea of guilty. A motion must be filed before the end of the sitting at which the sentence was imposed. 

Com. v. Burke, 16 Gray, 32

A defendant who has been tried on two counts of an indictment and the jury disagreed may be put on trial on another count of the same indictment, before the same jury and before the same court. 

Com. v. Porter, 10 Met. 263

Counsel for the defendant can address the jury on questions of law. See discussion by Shaw, C. J. 

Herrick v. Smith, 1 Gray, 1

When the proceedings are irregular and erroneous, the court or magistrate has jurisdiction, the judgment is voidable only and not void, and of course must stand good until reversed or annulled in the proper course of proceedings by a court having the authority to reverse or annul it. But where it appears on the face

CRIMINAL CASES 

459 of the proceeding that the magistrate had no jurisdiction, the proceedings are wholly void, the commitment is without authority, and the party committed is entitled to be discharged from his imprisonment without reversal of the judgment. 

Com. v. Bliss, 1 Mass. 32 

Com. v. Elliott, 2 Mass. 372

If the civil case is pending, the court will continue the criminal indictment, it being for the same cause. 

Com. v. Hearsey, 1 Mass. 137

The defendant pleaded guilty and the judgment was postponed. The court found that there was not any crime committed and ordered the complaint dismissed.

Pleading second count not prejudicial as to second conviction. 

Com. v. Makarian, 250 Mass. 211

The defendant held not to be prejudiced by reading of the allegation of a prior conviction in the complaint in view of the charge. 

Power of court after disposition. 

Smith v. District Court, 246 Mass. at 196 

Com. v. Weymouth, 2 Allen, 144

When a case has been disposed of finally, the further power of the court to deal with it, save in the ways provided for by statute, is gone. Exceptions. 

Com. v. Blinn, 219 Mass. 386

The Supreme Court can decide only cases that come to it from the Superior Court and after a person has been sentenced. 

Com. v. Cronin, 245 Mass. 163

A justice of the Superior Court cannot report a case to the Supreme Court until the defendant has been convicted. The authority under Chapter 231, Section 111, of the General Laws refers only to civil cases.

Attachment of property in the hands of the officer. 

Morris v. Penniman, 14 Gray, 220

A watch delivered without objection by one arrested for larceny of other property cannot be attached while in the hands of the officer.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Psychopathic examination. 

Com. v. Vallarelli, 273 Mass. 240

The fact that the defendant was not examined according to Section 100 of Chapter 123 of the General Laws does not of right entitle the defendant to a new trial. The fact that the clerk of the court did not know of the records of the defendant under the above act is not material to the defendant. 

Com. v. Soaris, 275 Mass. 291 

Com. v. Spencer, 212 Mass. 438

The Superior Court can commit the defendant to the Psychopathic Hospital before his trial for 60 days. A report must be filed within 30 days. Bail. 

Com. v. Trask, 15 Mass. 277

Where one is incarcerated for dangerously wounding another so that his life is in danger the defendant may be kept in jail without bail until the danger is over. 

Hanlon's Case, 119 Mass. 59

When a defendant is surrendered by his surety, the court can order him committed to jail until the further order of the court. 

The court must order him to furnish a new bond for his appearance and on his not doing so the court can then order him committed. 

Whitton v. Harding, 15 Mass. 535

If a surety surrenders his principal in court personally, it must be made a matter of record and in writing. 

Evidence not under oath. 

Sylvester v. Com., 253 Mass. 244

The defendant was a minor charged with being a delinquent child. An informal plea of not guilty was entered. Evidence was received from the complainant, not being under oath. The defendant was notified of his right to appeal. The verdict was good. 

Failure to make a complaint. 

Com. v. Tobin, 108 Mass. 426

The omission of an officer to make a complaint against the defendant for the crime that he was arrested for is no defense to

CRIMINAL CASES 

461 a complaint which he subsequently complained of him for, arising out of the same disturbance.

Signature by initials. 

Webber v. Davis, 5 Allen, 593

If a magistrate signs his name by his surname in full and his given name by initials, it is sufficient.. 

Wrong defendant pleading to complaint.

Com. v. Capland, 254 Mass. 556 

An indictment charged one Frederick Doe for violation of the liquor law. Someone appeared three days later and pleaded not guilty. One month later man who was not the man who had previously appeared refused to plead, the court ordered a plea of not guilty to be entered, the court finding that the second man was the one charged as Frederick Doe. Held that the first plea was entered by mistake and was properly vacated. 

Possession what is? 

Com. v. Homer, 235 Mass. 526

A thing is in the possession of a person in respect to a robbery which is within his reach, inspection, observation, or control, that he could, if not overcome by violence or prevented by fear, retain the possession of it. When the owner is kept in one room in a house and is forced to tell where his property may be found in another room, and the assailant goes there and takes the property, such a taking is robbery. 

Com. v. Weiner, 255 Mass. 506

If goods are in the possession of a clerk in a store and he is attacked and robbed, the indictment which alleges the ownership of the goods in the true owner is good. The indictment could have alleged the possession in the hands of the clerk.

Objection in the Superior Court. 

Thompson v. Kenney, 110 Mass. 317

After a trial in the Superior Court the defendant cannot raise the point that the justices of the lower court had no jurisdiction. 

Com. v. Brusie, 145 Mass. 117

An objection to the form of the warrant issued for the arrest of an accused person by the justice of a district court for an of-

CRIMINAL CASES 

fense within the jurisdiction of that court, cannot be taken for the first time in the Superior Court on appeal. 

Docket entries admissible. 

Savage v. Welch, 246 Mass. at 176

Docket entries are admissible. They are a part of the record of the court. They import incontrovertible verity and cannot be contradicted. They stand as final. The only method of their correction is by an order of the court. Attestation. 

Com. v. Ford, 14 Gray, 399

A copy of the complaint, warrant, officer's return, and a record of the proceedings being on the same paper, an attestation of the paper is sufficient. 

Com. v. Waitt, 131 Mass. 417

An attestation by a magistrate at the end of a record was a sufficient attestation of all the proceedings included in the complaint and warrant. An objection to the form of the warrant when the accused is before the court is not open to objection by the defendant. 

Legislature can subpoena witnesses. 

Atty. Gen. v. Brissenden, 271 Mass. 172

The General Court has the right to make investigations in order to ascertain facts as a basis of legislation and for that purpose can subpoena witnesses and compel them to testify. 

Computing time - Sundays. 

Marcellina, Pet'r, 271 Mass. 323

When the time is limited by a statute for the performance of any act for less than seven days, Sundays shall not be included. 

In filing exceptions Sundays and holidays shall not be included. 

Costs taxed against commonwealth. 

Com. v. Evans, 10 Gray, 463 

Walsh v. Com., 224 Mass. 39 

Britton v. Com., 1 Cush. 302

The costs must be taxed against the commonwealth when the defendant in the Supreme Court has his exceptions allowed.

CRIMINAL CASES 

463 

Restitution of stolen property. 

Com. v. Boudrie, 4 Gray, 418

The right of the owner of stolen property to a summary restitution thereof is limited to the articles stolen, and does not extend to the money into which they have been changed by the thief. 

Costs and travel while in prison. 

Britton v. Com., 1 Cush. 302 

Where the Supreme Court reversed a decision the defendant is entitled to costs and travel, being a part of the legal costs, and he is entitled to the same although he remained and was confined in jail. 

Torn indictment. 

Com. v. Roland, 97 Mass. 598

An indictment accidentally torn which may be reunited without the omission of any material word is sufficient for further legal proceedings. 

Demurrer by district attorney on a plea in bar. 

Com. v. Bosworth, 113 Mass. 200

If a district attorney demurs to a plea of former acquittal, he admits the truth of the allegations. 

Fine paid. Exceptions argued. 

Com. v. Fleckner, 167 Mass. 13

The defendant was sentenced to pay a fine and the fine was paid. Subsequently the exceptions were argued. The paying of the fine was not a consent to the sentence but a payment under duress. 

Justice illegally holding office. 

Com. v. Taber, 123 Mass. 253

A justice who is at the same time the mayor of a city is a justice de facto, and his jurisdiction cannot be tried in a proceeding to which he was not a party.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Ouster of an official from office. 

Com. v. Allen, 128 Mass. 308

An information by the Attorney General in the name of the commonwealth is the proper process for ouster of a person who holds office de facto and not de jure. A public office is vacated only by the death or resignation or removal of the incumbent. 

Cannot try defendant on a search warrant. A complaint must be made. 

Shepard Mfg. Co., 217 Mass. 444

After a search warrant has been served and returned into court with the doings of the officer duly recorded, a complaint must be issued for the defendant, to which he must plead. He cannot be arraigned on the search warrant. 

Court of common pleas. 

Brien v. Com., 5 Met. 508

The office of the judge of the municipal court as a distinct and independent office was virtually abolished by Statutes 1843, Chapter 7, which transferred its duties to the justices of the court of common pleas. Interpreters. 

In re Norberg, 4 Mass. 81

Where a witness ignorant of the English language renders an interpreter necessary, the interpreter should be sworn. 

Rules of the court. 

Pratt v. Pratt, 157 Mass. 503

Rules of procedure adopted by a court have the force of law, and must be strictly adhered to, by the court as well as by parties litigant. 

Thompson v. Hatch, 3 Pick. 512

Courts may modify or rescind their rules or may, in establishing them, reserve the right of discretion for particular cases; but a rule made without such qualifications must be applied to all cases that fall within it, until it is rescinded. 

Baker v. Blood, 128 Mass. 543

A rule of court in which no discretionary power is reserved cannot be dispensed with by a single judge.

CRIMINAL CASES 

465 

Docket of court. 

Good v. French, 115 Mass. 201

The docket of the municipal court is the record of proceedings in that court, until the full record is made up, and is sufficient proof of the proceedings therein. 

Nature and scope of general jurisdiction. 

Com. v. Johnson, 8 Mass. 87

No court in this state has any jurisdiction, original or appellate, but such as given to it either expressly or by necessary and inevitable implication. 

Prisoner under arrest. 

Williams v. Blunt, 2 Mass. 207

Where one was imprisoned by lawful authority from a magistrate, the court of common pleas has no jurisdiction to issue a writ of “de homine replegiando" in the form prescribed by the statute for one "held without order of law." The Latin phrase means "for replevying a man"; it is now superseded by a writ of habeas corpus.

PRESUMPTION 

George v. Clark, 186 Mass. 426 

Whippen v. Whippen, 147 Mass. 294

The unexplained absence of a person unheard of for seven years causes a presumption of his death. 

Talbot, Pet'r, 250 Mass. 517 

Com. v. Hayden, 163 Mass. 453

Absence alone, no matter how long continued, is not sufficient to raise the presumption of death. The absence must be from the person's domicile or his established residence. A person leaving his house for temporary purposes of business or pleasure and not being heard from or known to be living for a term of seven years, the presumption of death arises. This presumption may be rebutted by counter evidence or by conflicting presumption. 

Com. v. De Francesco, 248 Mass. 9

The presumption of innocence of the accused at the trial on a complaint or an indictment is not a matter of evidence.

CRIMINAL CASES PRINCIPAL IN THE SECOND DEGREE 

Com. v. Chapman, 11 Cush. 422

If the defendant is present and aiding and abetting the felony, so as to make him a principal in the second degree, that evidence would support an indictment charging him as the sole principal, as having struck the blow with his own hand. 

PROBABLE CAUSE GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 276, SEC. 42 GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 218, SEC. 30 

Barnes v. Ryan, 174 Mass. 117 

Bacon v. Towne, 4 Cush. 238

On probable cause, while nothing is said in the statute as to the right of the court to discharge the accused, this is naturally to be understood where the evidence is insufficient to show probable cause to hold him. But no power is given the court to acquit him, or to render judgment in his favor which could be pleaded res judicata. The court has no power to try the accused but only to give him a hearing and then to bind him over to the Superior 

Court or discharge him. 

Com. v. Goldstein, 180 Mass. 374

Examination waived. Although it has the same effect as to the finding of probable cause to believe the defendant guilty, it is not such an admission of probable cause as to preclude action for malicious prosecution. 

Com. v. Sullivan, 156 Mass. 487 

Com. v. Hamilton, 129 Mass. 479

A man should not be subjected to two complaints on the same matter, but even in a criminal case this may be done, where the court discharges a man for want of probable cause to hold him. 

PROBATION OFFICER 

Com. v. McGovern, 183 Mass. 238

A defendant may be surrendered at any time while he is on probation, and the probation officer, even if he is acting wrongfully, the defendant being before the court, the court can inquire into all the facts and sentence.

CRIMINAL CASES 

467 

O'Brien v. City of Boston, 266 Mass. 156

A probation officer is appointed for his duties as such, and if he performs his duties and the duties of another probation officer, while the latter is on a vacation, he is not entitled to extra wages. 

Finer v. Com., 250 Mass. 493

The defendant was sentenced on a suspended sentence and placed on probation without any definite time set for the period of probation; was subsequently surrendered and sentenced on the original sentence. Although the statutes state that a definite term is required, the fact that there was not any does not affect the sentence and the suspended sentence, which was legal. When a case is pending in a court of general jurisdiction in which continuances are from term to term, the defendant may waive the formal entrance for continuance and consent that the case may remain in court without such entries until asked for by either party.

PROVISIONS 

Com. v. Lutton, 157 Mass. 392 

Oleomargarine and butter are provisions. "Provisions" means all articles of food. 

Com. v. Reid, 175 Mass. 325 

Green v. City of Fitchburg, 219 Mass. 121

Ice is not provisions under the hawker and peddler statute. 

PUBLIC AMUSEMENTS GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 140, SEC. 181 

Com. v. Bow, 177 Mass. 347

A merry-go-round so called, maintained in an enclosure opening on a public street, where music is furnished without charge, but a charge is made for riding on the flying horses, is a public amusement requiring a license. 

Com. v. Quinn, 164 Mass. 11

A dance hall to which the public are admitted upon the payment of a small fee is a public amusement.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Gee, 6 Cush. 174

Setting up a public amusement without a license does not apply to a school for the teaching of dancing, although admission is charged for each evening. 

Jaffrian v. B'l'd. Com'r, 275 Mass. 267

A miniature golf course is a public amusement within the meaning of General Laws, Chapter 140, Section 181, and subject to the regulations of police power. 

Roys v. Johnson, 7 Gray, 162

An actor may maintain an action for his services in an unlicensed theatrical exhibition, unless it appears that he knew that his employer had no license. 

Com. v. Alexander, 185 Mass. 551

A religious or charitable society lawfully may give a public vaudeville entertainment on the Lord's day if the excess of the receipts over the expenses is to be devoted exclusively to a charitable or religious purpose, the word "proceeds" as used in the statute meaning the net returns of the entertainment after the payment of necessary expenses. 

Com. v. McGann, 213 Mass. 213

In all cities outside of Boston, for a public entertainment the chief of the district police as well as the mayor must each issue a license. The license may be revoked at the discretion of either. 

Whitcomb v. Vigeant, 240 Mass. 359

The statute providing for the licensing of public amusements is broad enough in its scope to include the licensing of public dances and dance halls. The use of a hall in a city for public dances in accordance with a license granted by the mayor under Section 181 cannot be a nuisance and could not subject the owner to injunction or indictment. 

Opinion of the justices, 247 Mass. 583

The act providing an additional charge of fifty cents on all tickets of admission by a ticket speculator is constitutional. 

In reference to the above a violation of Section 185D of Chapter 140 was declared by Zottoli, J., to be unconstitutional.

469 

PUBLIC OFFICER 

Com. v. Lennon, 172 Mass. 434

The facts of one acting as a public officer is no defense to a complaint for obstructing a sidewalk with furniture which he as a constable was removing from a house. 

Com. v. Gearing, 1 Allen, 595

The acts of a public officer are presumed to have been done rightly until the contrary is shown. One de jure cannot be removed from office in a proceeding instituted directly for that purpose. De facto, can be removed in such a proceeding. Until a de facto officer is removed in such a proceeding, his acts are as valid as a de jure officer. 

Atty. Gen. v. Drohan, 169 Mass. 534 

Membership in a political committee belonging to a political party is not a public office. A public officer is one whose duties are in their nature public, that is, involving in their own performance the exercise of some portion of the sovereign power, whether great or small, and in whose proper performance all citizens, irrespective of party, are interested, either as members of the entire body politic or of some duly established division of it. 

Com. v. Bird, 264 Mass. 485 

Com. v. Tsaffaras, 250 Mass. 445

An examiner of the Registrar of Motor Vehicles is an executive officer. As to Public Institutions officer, see above. 

Com. v. Dorr, 216 Mass. 314

The official record of a fact made by a public officer in the performance of his duties may be introduced in evidence as to proof of the truth of the facts recorded.

PUBLIC RECORDS 

Com. v. Seggee, 218 Mass. 501

Chapter 233, Sections 74, 75, 76of the General Laws copies of all public records shall be competent equally with the originals. The originals are also competent.

CRIMINAL CASES 

The introduction of public records such as the analysis of liquor, weather reports, and the like, do not violate the constitution that a defendant is entitled to meet his accusers face to face. 

Discussion as to when they are not admissible. 

PUNCTUATION 

Com. v. Grant, 201 Mass. 458

Punctuation is not conclusive in determining the meaning of writings; at most it is only an aid in their construction. 

Com. v. Kelley, 177 Mass. 221

Semicolon law, so called. Punctuation may be disregarded. It may be resorted to as an aid in construction when it tends to throw light on its meaning.

RAILER AND BRAWLER GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 272, SEC. 53 

Com. v. Foley, 99 Mass. 497

The defendant in his own dwelling house was in the habit of using violent and loud language and profanity so as to attract crowds in the street, also addressed to crowds in the street. Guilty. 

Stratton v. Com., 10 Met. 217

The complaint need not allege nor set out the specific acts of railing and brawling.

RAILROAD VIOLATIONS 

Com. v. Killian, 109 Mass. 345

A passenger who pulls a danger signal attached to a passenger train, from whatever motive, is not guilty of obstructing a railroad train and endangering the lives of the passengers. 

Vermont v. Mass. St. Ry., 108 Mass. 7

None of the conditions printed on the back of a railroad ticket can relieve the railroad of any legal effect under a penal statute for gross negligence or carelessness.

CRIMINAL CASES 

471 

Com. v. Carroll, 145 Mass. 403

Throwing missiles at a railroad car. It does not make any difference whether the car is in use at the time or not. 

Com. v. McCarthy, 145 Mass. 575

An allegation that the defendant threw a stone at a railroad car is not supported by proof that he threw a billet of wood. 

On his wife.

RAPE 

GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 265, SEC. 22 

Com. v. Fogarty, 8 Gray, 489

A man may be convicted of rape on his wife who assisted another man to rape her. He may be complained of as a principal in the second degree. The pleader need not allege that the woman ravished was not the wife of the defendant when it is a person other than the defendant. "Violently and against her will feloniously did ravish and carnally know" sufficiently states that it was by force. 

Age of the female. 

Com. v. Sugland, 4 Gray, 7

An indictment which charges rape is sufficient without stating the age of the female. If the indictment alleges against her will, the offense is for a female over the age of consent. 

Com. v. Roosnell, 143 Mass. 32

The offense of unlawfully and carnally knowing and abusing a female child under the age of ten years is known and designated as rape. 

Com. v. Murphy, 165 Mass. 66 

One who has carnal knowledge of a girl under sixteen years of age is guilty of rape even if he does not know her age and she consents. 

Age of the defendant. 

Com. v. Scannell, 11 Cush. 547

The pleader need not allege that the defendant was over the age of fourteen years nor that the female was not his wife nor that the act was committed with force and arms or feloniously.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Green, 2 Pick. 380 

A boy under the age of fourteen years may be indicted for assault with intent to commit a rape. 

Character of the girl. 

Com. v. Harris, 131 Mass. 336

The character of the girl for chastity is a proper subject for inquiry as bearing on the possibility of her consent. Her general reputation of previous intercourse with the defendant is admissible, but not intercourse with third persons. 

Com. v. Tegan, 105 Mass. 393

It is not competent for the defendant to show either by crossexamination of the woman or by other evidence that she has declared herself pregnant by other men. Consent. 

Com. v. Burke, 105 Mass. 376

The expression "without her consent" used in reference to rape includes cases where the defendant acts with knowledge that the woman is insensible and incapable of consenting. In this case she was under the influence of liquor. Incest. 

Com. v. Goodhue, 43 Mass. 193

Where a daughter consents to intercourse with her father, the crime is incest and not rape. Evidence. 

Com. v. Doherty, 10 Cush. 52

The state can show the effect of the force used on the woman. 

Com. v. Merrill, 14 Gray, 415

Taking indecent liberties, lying down beside her, with his private parts exposed, putting one leg over her and trying to induce her to have sexual intercourse, is lewdness but not an intent to rape. 

Lesser verdict. 

Com. v. Drum, 19 Pick. 479

One indicted for rape may be convicted of assault and battery.

CRIMINAL CASES 

473 

Force used. 

Com. v. McDonald, 110 Mass. 405

The judge instructed the jury that the act of the defendant must have been without any consent on the woman's part; that the defendant must have used sufficient force to accomplish his purpose; and that the degree of resistance was frequently essential in determining whether the want of consent was real; but there was not any rule of law requiring the jury to be satisfied that the woman, according to their measure of strength, used all the physical force in opposition of which she was capable. 

Jurisdiction of defendant Age. 

Com. v. Dies, 248 Mass. 482

Under General Laws, Chapter 119, Section 52, a boy under seventeen years of age may be indicted, tried, and sentenced for the crime of rape, since by General Laws, Chapter 265, Section 22, the crime is punishable in the state prison for life or a term of years. The defendant was sixteen years of age when the offense was committed but was seventeen at the time of making his plea. Pleading. 

Com. v. Hackett, 170 Mass. 194

In and upon a child named, under the age of sixteen years, to wit, of the age of twelve years, feloniously did make an assault, and her the said child, then and there feloniously did unlawfully and carnally know and abuse. 

Com. v. Thompson, 116 Mass. 346

Rape necessarily includes an assault and battery. To sustain an indictment for assault with intent to commit a rape, it is not necessary to allege or prove a battery. But a battery may be one of the facts by which the offense is made out. It then constitutes a part, though not an essential part, of the offense which the statute defines and punishes. If not alleged, there is no variance; if alleged, there is no duplicity. Verdict. 

Com. v. Squires, 97 Mass. 59 

A complaint averring, in and upon one Margaret Morton an unmarried female of the age of ten years or more, to wit, of

CRIMINAL CASES 

the age of twelve years, violently and feloniously did make an assault, and her, the said Margaret, then and there by force and against her will, violently and feloniously did ravish and carnally know; he, the said defendant, then and there being a married man and there having a lawful wife alive other than the said Margaret. The jury returned that the defendant had carnal knowledge but not against her will. He was sentenced for adultery. "Carnal knowledge" means and implies sexual intercourse. 

Com. v. Creadon, 162 Mass. 466

A defendant can be convicted on an indictment charging an assault with intent to commit rape, if the evidence satisfies the jury that the crime was rape. 

Incriminating circumstances. 

Com. v. Bean, 137 Mass. 570

Evidence that the defendant a month before the assault invited the girl to walk in certain woods, and five weeks after the assault, followed her at night, is admissible in the judge's discretion. 

Com. v. Cleary, 172 Mass. 175

Evidence of the complaint of the ravished female, when not too remote in point of time, is admissible not as part of the res gestae, or as evidence of the substantive facts charged, or to disprove consent, but as tending to corroborate her testimony at the trial. Definition. 

Com. v. McCan, 277 Mass. 199 "Rape" is the carnal knowledge of a woman against her will or of a female child with or against her will; its essence being felonious and violent penetration of the person of the female. 

REASONABLE DOUBT 

Com. v. Kendall, 162 Mass. 221

A reasonable doubt is not a mere possible doubt. It is that state of the case which after the entire comparison and consideration of all the evidence, leaves the minds of the jurors in that condition that they cannot say they feel an abiding conviction, to a moral certainty, of the truth of the charge.

CRIMINAL CASES 

475 

RECEIVING STOLEN GOODS Pleading. 

GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 266, SEC. 60 

Com. v. Hogan, 121 Mass. 373 

Com. v. Slate, 11 Gray, 60

In a complaint the pleader need not name the thief, but if he does so, that fact must be proved. 

Stevens v. Com., 6 Met. 241

The buying, receiving, and aiding in the concealment of stolen property describes only one offense. 

Com. v. Lakemen, 5 Gray, 82

The indictment need not allege "taken and carried away" in a separate indictment for receiving stolen property; this is not essential. The same technical particularity is not required in stating the larceny as would be required in an indictment against the party committing the larceny. 

Com. v. Barry, 116 Mass. 1 

Com. v. Sullivan, 136 Mass. 170

The crime of receiving stolen property is a substantive crime in itself, and not merely accessorial to the principal crime of larceny, and in the complaint for receiving stolen property the pleader need not state the place of the larceny. 

O'Connell v. Com., 7. Met. 460

A complaint which alleges divers articles and their value is good, but the defendant pleaded guilty to receiving fifty dollars' worth of the said property, which is a void plea, and no judgment could be rendered on the verdict. 

Com. v. Harris, 232 Mass. 588

A complaint for conspiracy to receive stolen goods, alleging the crimes to be committed as time occurs in the future, is good pleading.

Evidence. 

Com. v. Kronick, 196 Mass. 286 

One may be convicted even if he received the property innocently, if afterwards, with the guilty knowledge that it was stolen, he aided in the concealment of it. If he bought it or aided

CRIMINAL CASES 

in its concealment, he can be convicted even if he acted only as an agent. 

Com. v. Kuperstein, 207 Mass. 25

The defendant need not have physical possession of the property, and in a complaint for aiding and concealing, no actual hiding is necessary, if the defendant put the stolen property in a position so as to lose its identity. 

Com. v. Phelps, 192 Mass. 591

Evidence of possession of property outside the state, stolen within the state, is not sufficient to warrant a conviction, but is admissible in connection with other evidence to show that the defendant received property within the state. 

Com. v. Peopcik, 251 Mass. 369

Although there was not any direct testimony that the defendant had knowledge that the property was stolen, the question whether the defendant received the property knowing it to be stolen was a question of fact for the jury. He may be convicted if he believed they were stolen in fact, although he may not have full and exact knowledge. 

Com. v. Bean, 117 Mass. 141

It is no defense that the defendant, knowing them to have been stolen and to aid in concealing them, received them as a friendly act, without benefit to himself or to any intent to receive benefit. 

Com. v. Jenkins, 10 Gray, 485

Evidence is admissible of conversation between the defendant and the thief before the commission of the offense, making arrangements for receiving the property. 

Com. v. Elisha, 3 Gray, 460 The record of the conviction of the thief on his plea of guilty of stealing certain property is not admissible to prove the theft, on the trial for the receiving of the property on an indictment against him alone which does not aver that the thief has been convicted. 

Com. v. Leonard, 140 Mass. 473

The defendant must know that the goods were stolen, but he does not need to know the hour nor day they were stolen; he must undoubtedly have notice which would put him on his guard as knowledge that the goods were acquired and turned

CRIMINAL CASES 

477 over to him by a person not taking them by mistake, nor by right, but taking them as thieves take them, that is, for the purpose of defrauding the owners and cheating them out of their property. 

Com. v. Grossman, 261 Mass. 68

It is not necessary to a conviction for buying, receiving, and aiding in the concealment of stolen goods, knowing them to have been stolen, that it be proved that the defendant received the goods from the person who stole them. 

Com. v. Donaruma, 260 Mass. 233

Evidence that the man from whom the defendant stated that he purchased the automobile was acquitted of larceny held inadmissible. 

Com. v. King, 9 Cush. 284

In an indictment against one for larceny at common law, and against another for receiving the goods so stolen, if the evidence fails to prove the larceny by the former, the charge of receiving stolen goods cannot be sustained against the latter. 

Com. v. Finn, 108 Mass. 466

The complaint may lay the money as the property of the person from whom it was stolen although he himself stole it from the true owner.

RECOGNIZANCE 

See under Bail and Bond. 

To impeach a witness.

RECORDS 

Fay v. Harlan, 128 Mass. 244

The record of a criminal court showing that a person was indicted for an offense to which he pleaded guilty and that he was discharged on probation is not admissible in evidence to impeach his credibility as a witness. The word "conviction" used in the above sense means the judgment of the court, and a plea of guilty without such final judgment is not sufficient. 

Olszewski v. Goldberg, 223 Mass. 27

A record of conviction following a plea of nolo contendere cannot be used to affect the credibility as a witness of the person so convicted.

CRIMINAL CASES 

White v. Creamer, 175 Mass. 567

While a sentence imposed after a plea of nolo contendere amounts to a conviction in that case in which the plea was entered, a record showing such conviction is not admissible in another case to show that the defendant was guilty. 

Com. v. Gorham, 99 Mass. 420

An indictment was placed on file. It afterwards was brought forward and was pending. The word "conviction" as used for impeaching a witness in any proceeding, civil or criminal, in court or before a person having authority to receive evidence, implies a judgment of the court. Not admissible. tion. 

Com. v. Meehan, 170 Mass. 362

Docket entries not extended are admissible to show convic- 

Com. v. Danton, 243 Mass. 552

Conviction of a witness of a crime to affect his credibility must be shown by the record. A conviction in the lower court from which an appeal was taken cannot be introduced for the purpose of contradicting the defendant. 

Com. v. Gallagher, 126 Mass. 54 

Com. v. Galligan, 155 Mass. 54

A record of a prior conviction when introduced for the purpose of impeaching credibility is final. The word "record" is conclusive and cannot be explained. 

Com. v. Haywood, 247 Mass. 16

A plea of guilty is admissible in a subsequent case. It is not a confession of guilt. Admissible only on the trial of a like indictment. 

Com. v. Stevenson, 127 Mass. 446

As to introduction of a divorce record proving adultery. Not admissible. 

Com. v. Ford, 146 Mass. 131

The conviction of either a felony or a misdemeanor may be shown to affect the credibility of a witness. 

Com. v. Ponzi, 256 Mass. 159

The record of conviction in the federal court can be introduced in the state court if the defendant takes the stand.

CRIMINAL CASES 

479 

Com. v. Bonner, 97 Mass. 587

A defendant in a criminal case may be asked for the purpose of affecting his credibility if he has been in the House of Correction, provided objection is waived that the record is the best evidence. 

Com. v. Brooks, 9 Gray, 299

The record of conviction cannot be proved by an unofficial copy of the clerk's docket supported by the oath of the copyist. 

As against the defendant. 

Com. v. Crekorian, 264 Mass. 94

A complaint to which the defendant pleaded guilty before a committing magistrate is admissible against the defendant at the trial on the indictment. The fact that such a plea is offered in another and distinct prosecution does not render it irrelevant and the less admissible. It must be an exemplified record. Under the plea of guilty the case was placed on file. 

Com. v. Feldman, 131 Mass. 588

On a trial for assault and battery on an officer committed while the defendant was under arrest for drunkenness, a record of the conviction and sentence of the defendant for drunkenness at the time of arrest is conclusive of fact. 

Com. v. Williams, 244 Mass. 515

Records were produced in court when the defendant was asked whether he had been previously convicted. They were marked but not taken to the jury room when they were deliberating. 

Admissible notwithstanding. 

Com. v. Lincoln, 110 Mass. 410

The defendant was charged with assault and battery on one S. Lincoln; introduced a record that Lincoln assaulted him (the defendant) on the same day to show self-defense. Only admissible to affect credibility. It was res inter alios. 

Labrie v. Milwood, 273 Mass. 578

Suit for alienation. The plaintiff called the defendant as a witness and introduced a record of adultery with the wife of the plaintiff. It is inadmissible under Sections 21, 22, or 23 of Chapter 233 of the General Laws.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Court records. 

Tufts v. Hancock, 171 Mass. 148

Court records cannot be controlled by parol evidence. 

Watts v. Stevenson, 169 Mass. 61

Oral evidence is incompetent to contradict the records of the court. 

Haskell v. Cunningham, 221 Mass. 49

A judicial record is conclusive and cannot be controlled by parol evidence. 

Merrill v. Kaulbeck, 158 Mass. 328

The court has the power to amend its records even after the lapse of a long time, and the record as amended imports absolute verity. 

Com. v. Quigley, 170 Mass. 14

Certified records need not be under seal nor bear the words "true copy attest" or that the clerk is the clerk of the court. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 135 Mass. 519

A court record that proceedings were duly conducted cannot be contradicted by oral testimony. 

Bornstein v. Justices, 269 Mass. 515

A record of a district court as returned must be taken as true. 

It imports uncontrovertible verity. 

Gloucester v. County Com'r., 116 Mass. 581

A court has the power to amend its records according to the truth. 

Good v. French, 115 Mass. 201

The proceedings of the municipal court are sufficiently proved by the docket, which is the record until the more full record is made up. 

Com. v. Taylor, 113 Mass. 1

Mere clerical omissions in records of courts, even in criminal prosecutions, can be supplied by amendments. 

Kelley v. Thomas, 15 Gray, 192

The committing officer can amend the warrant of the commitment of a prisoner.

CRIMINAL CASES 

481 

Kelley v. Dresser, 11 Allen, 31

The truth of a record in a criminal case of the magistrate's record determined by him cannot be impeached even in an action against him for fraudulently and corruptly altering the complaint and the warrant, after the same has been served. A record imports absolute verity and that no averment plea or proof is admissible to the contrary, and this is true though it bears the marks of interlineations. 

Willis v. Stevens, 2 Gray, 115

Oral testimony cannot be offered to vary or contradict written judicial records. It is necessarily a written memorial and cannot exist partly in writing and partly in parol. The defendant appealed and claimed his appeal was not allowed and the record did not show the same. 

Chamberlain v. Ball, 15 Gray, 352

A copy of the record of the court attested by the clerk need not be under seal. To be admissible it need not be exemplified. 

Com. v. Phillips, 11 Pick. 28

A clerical copy of the record introduced in evidence may be amended at any time before the case is given to the jury. 

Lost record. 

Sayles v. Briggs, 4 Met. 421

A lost record may be proved by parol evidence and that the minutes may be introduced where the record has not been drawn out in extenso as containing the elements of a record and in truth for the time being the record itself. The loss must be proved, however. 

Death certificate. 

Warren v. De Costa, 269 Mass. 415

A death certificate is a public record, a proper proof of which is a certified record and copy. 

Who may attest records. 

Com. v. Chase, 6 Cush. 248

The clerk of a city or town is the proper certifying officer to authenticate copies of ordinances or bylaws. Such copies are admissible when purporting to be duly attested without any certification of the signature of the clerk.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Of an appointed person. 

Robbins v. Town of Townsend, 20 Pick. 345

The certificate of the City Clerk of Boston setting forth that A had been appointed to a certain office and still continues to hold it is not competent to establish the fact as such. It is a certificate of fact and not a certificate of records. 

Record of previous crime connected with same crime. 

Com. v. Evans, 101 Mass. 25

On a trial for manslaughter a record can be introduced of the conviction of the defendant for the assault which later caused the death, and is conclusive evidence that the assault was unjustifiable. The assault occurred on August 8, 1867, and the death occurred the following October. The latter is not a bar.

REFORMATORY PRISON FOR WOMEN 

Beard v. City of Boston, 151 Mass. 96

The Reformatory Prison for Women is not a state prison for women.

RELIGIOUS BELIEF 

Com. v. Smith, 2 Gray, 516

The want of religious belief in a witness cannot be shown by the examination of the witness himself. 

Thurston v. Whitney, 2 Cush. 104

One who does not believe in the existence of God is not a competent witness, and this may be proved by the testimony of witnesses. Discussion by Wilde, C. J., on atheism. 

Com. v. Hills, 10 Cush. 530

Question whether a person is or is not an atheist is a question of fact for the presiding justice. 

Com. v. Burke, 16 Gray, 32

Cannot be examined as to his religious belief either upon voir dire or cross-examination.

RELIGIOUS FREEDOM 

Moustakis v. Greek Church Society, 261 Mass. 462

The courts cannot inquire into questions exclusively ecclesiastical, for the reasons that religious freedom is the constitutional

CRIMINAL CASES 

483 right of all citizens, under our government, and for the further reason that, if the courts should deal with litigation of that nature, the whole subject of doctrinal theology, the customs, canonical laws, both written and unwritten, and the fundamental organization of the various religious denominations would need to be examined with care for the purpose of reaching authoritative conclusions.

REVISED ORDINANCES 

O'Brien v. City of Woburn, 184 Mass. 598

Revised ordinances must be attested; the courts cannot take judicial notice. General Laws, Chapter 233, Sections 74, 75, 76. 

City of Boston v. Coon, 175 Mass. 283

Revised ordinances are only admissible in evidence when signed by the city clerk. 

Maher v. Steuer, 170 Mass. 454

Courts other than municipal courts do not take judicial notice of bylaws or city ordinances. 

Com. v. McCafferty, 145 Mass. 384

As a condition precedent to the validity of a city ordinance no publication is necessary. 

Com. v. Maletsky, 203 Mass. 241

An ordinance which attempts to vest in a city council or board of control or some administrative officer of the municipality, the power, not subject to review by the courts, or by other higher authority, to permit or refuse to permit the carrying on of a business lawful in itself and not prohibited by legislation is not to be sustained. 

Com. v. Turner, 1 Cush. 493

A statute which authorizes towns to make bylaws cannot make them in conflict with statutory authority. 

Com. v. Lennon, 172 Mass. 434

Acting as a public officer is no defense to a complaint for violation of a revised ordinance. 

Com. v. Rawson, 183 Mass. 491

A revised ordinance which is adopted when there exists a board of health is invalid.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Slocum, 230 Mass. 180

A revised ordinance for operating motor vehicles for passengers for hire without a license is constitutional. 

Gordon, Pet'r, 16 Pick. 504

One requiring snow and ice to be removed is constitutional. 

Com. v. Cutter, 156 Mass. 52 "Suffering to remain" implies an opportunity to remove and a failure to do so. 

Clinton v. Welch, 166 Mass. 133

Allowing snow and ice to remain on the sidewalk, the defendant was sentenced to forfeit $10. The fact that the penalty is more than the town can recover on a suit is a valid penalty. 

Com. v. Cutter, 156 Mass. 52

Cities and towns can adopt bylaws and ordinances as a police power. 

Com. v. Beck, 194 Mass. 14

An ordinance which requires wagons used for the conveyance of goods from place to place within a city applies to an express company which receives goods from other cities on trains and delivers them to a place within the city. 

Com. v. Goodnow, 117 Mass. 114

A statute prohibiting overhanging bay windows is a valid one. 

Pedrick v. Bailey, 12 Gray, 161

Providing that no person shall maintain an awning before his door is constitutional. 

Vandin, Pet'r, 6 Pick. 187

Prohibiting any person not licensed from removing house offal and dirt from the city is not in restraint of trade and is a valid ordinance.

RIOT 

COMMON LAW 

Hathaway v. City of Everett, 205 Mass. 246

A city is not liable to a citizen who is assaulted by one of its police officers.

CRIMINAL CASES 

485 

Yalenenzian v. City of Boston, 238 Mass. 538
