Statutory construction of a riot. What constitutes a riot. 

Property destroyed by rioters, the city is liable under a special statute. If property is carried away or stolen, that right does not exist. 

Com. v. Frishman, 235 Mass. 449

Description and form of complaint at common law. It is not necessary that the act of violence be committed. Those acting in consort, those present and assisting, may be charged as principals. 

It is not necessary that the official named in the statute should order rioters to disperse, this being common law. In the complaint it is not necessary to allege an assault, it is incidental and not an essential part of the offense. 

Com. v. Gibney, 2 Allen, 150

An indictment for riot must charge an unlawful assembling by the defendants. 

Com. v. Berry, 5 Gray, 93

A riot cannot be committed by one person. It is by acting in concert, the unlawful combination, which constitutes the offense. 

An indictment against three for a riot is supported on the separate trial of one, by proof of a riot in which he and two others joined. 

Com. v. Runnells, 10 Mass. 518

To disturb another in the lawful right is a trespass, and if done by numbers unlawfully assembled, the same act is a riot. It is sufficient to allege the defendants assembled with force and arms and being so assembled did then and there, etc.

ROAD 

See also under Highway. 

Com. v. Gammons, 40 Mass. 201

A passageway on a wharf which has never been laid out as a highway nor a town way, in which the public had no right except by sufferance of the owners, is not a road within the meaning of the law of the road. The term "road" is not necessarily limited to a public way.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Pleading.

ROBBERY GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 265, SECS. 17-21 

Com. v. Gallagher, 6 Met. 565

An indictment which alleges that the defendant assaulted and robbed A, and being armed with a dangerous weapon did strike and wound A, is not proved by evidence that the defendant put a slight scratch on the face of A, not as to the striking by evidence that the defendant put his arms around the neck of A and threw him to the ground. 

Com. v. Mowry, 11 Allen, 20

In an indictment for robbery while armed with a dangerous weapon and wounding and striking the person robbed, it is not necessary to aver or prove that the wounding and striking were done with the dangerous weapon. 

Com. v. Humphries, 7 Mass. 242

A larceny committed with force and violence or with a constructive force by any assault and putting in fear is a robbery, and in an indictment for such an offense an allegation of force and violence is sufficient, without alleging that the person robbed was put in fear. 

Com. v. Clifford, 8 Cush. 215

The pleader must aver in the indictment that the property taken must be the property of the person robbed or of a third person, and that person was put in fear, and these facts must be alleged. If the pleader uses the phrase "force and violence," the allegation of putting in fear is not necessary. 

Com. v. Cody, 165 Mass. 133

Form of complaint when the defendant is armed. Definition. 

Com. v. Weiner, 255 Mass. 506

Robbery is the exercise of force actual and constructive against another in order to take personal property of any value whatsoever with the intention of stealing from the protection which the person of that other affords. That the force is within a building rather than on the highway is immaterial. The pleader

CRIMINAL CASES 

487 can allege the property to be in the possession of either the clerk in the store who was robbed or in the actual owner. 

Snatching bills. 

Com. v. Ordway, 12 Cush. 270

Snatching bills from the owner's hand and thereby touching his hand but with no intention of injuring or touching his person, is not an assault with violence. Possession. 

Com. v. Homer, 235 Mass. 526

A thing is in the possession of a person in respect to a robbery which is within his reach, inspection, observation or control, that he could, if not overcome by violence or prevented by fear, retain his possession of it. The complainant while in fear sent her maid for her jewelry and delivered it to the defendant. Held it was enough if it was under her protection or control and that by violence or putting in fear she was compelled to surrender it. 

When continuing and murder is done. 

Com. v. Devereaux, 256 Mass. 387

If a robbery had begun and was taking place at the time the murder was committed, it is murder in the first degree. If the murder took place after the robbery was committed, this would not be so. Two defendants sat in an automobile after committing the robbery, while the third defendant killed a man who attempted to interfere and intercept him on the way out to join his confederates. The robbery was not then over. They were still in possession of the stolen money and the plan had not been completed. .Com. v. Martin, 17 Mass. 359

Robbery while being armed and intending to kill is a capital case.

Pleading. 

Com. v. Cahill, 12 Allen, 540

An indictment for robbery of sundry bank bills and United States treasury notes all together amounting to ninety dollars and of the value of ninety dollars, cannot be supported by proof of a robbery of United States notes alone. The value taken by robbery, however, is immaterial by the terms of our statute as

CRIMINAL CASES 

well as at common law; it must be "money or other property" which may be subject of larceny. 

Com. v. Holmes, 165 Mass. 457 

Confining and putting in fear complaint for attempting to commit larceny of property in a building. 

Com. v. Griffiths, 126 Mass. 252 

Charging the robbery of several "promissory notes then and there of the currency in said commonwealth" is sustained by proof that the notes stolen were either bank-bills or treasury notes. The words "of the currency current in this commonwealth" are equivalent to "current as money in this commonwealth." 

SEAL OF THE COMMONWEALTH 

Com. v. Sherman Mfg. Co., 189 Mass. 76

No one can acquire as against the commonwealth the right to use the arms of the great seal as a trade mark. This prohibition is constitutional. 

SEAMEN 

Com. v. Bartlett, 190 Mass. 148

United States Code 4520 and 4523 says a seaman does not become a member of the crew of a vessel until he and the master have signed articles.

SCIRE FACIAS 

Com. v. Downey, 9 Mass. 520

A scire facias can only issue from the court in possession of the record upon which it issues. 

SECOND OFFENSE 

Tuttle v. Com., 2 Gray, 505 

Walsh v. Com., 224 Mass. 39 

Flaherty v. Thomas, 12 Allen, at 432

In order to punish a defendant for a second offense the allegation must be specially pleaded. 

Com. v. Miller, 8 Gray, 484

When so pleaded, it must be proved by a copy of the record of conviction.

CRIMINAL CASES 

489 

Com. v. Fortier, 258 Mass. 98

On a plea of guilty need not prove second offense by record. 

The defendant on cross-examination admitted that he had pleaded guilty to the former offense. 

Com. v. Daley, 4 Gray, 209

The state must prove the first conviction was before the commission of the second offense. 

Garvey v. Com., 8 Gray, 382

One cannot be sentenced for a second offense unless the fact that it is a second offense was alleged in the complaint. 

Com. v. Holley, 3 Gray, 458

If a description of the second offense neglects to state before what court the defendant was convicted, this may be amended in court so as to conform to the record, without the formality of sending the same back to find a new indictment. 

Com. v. Barney, 258 Mass. 609

Where at the trial in the Superior Court of a complaint charging a second offense of operating under the influence of liquor there was evidence of but a single offense, but no evidence to support that part of the complaint which charged a second offense, it was proper for the court to instruct the jury to disregard that part of the complaint and to consider the case only for a first offense. 

Com. v. Markarian, 250 Mass. 211

On a complaint for exposing and keeping liquor for sale, a second offense was pleaded. The allegation in regard to the earlier conviction was not a description or a characterization of the offense charged but was form only and could be treated as surplusage. It was a formal defect and should have been taken advantage of, since it was not a statement of fact necessary to be proven to make that act complained of a crime. The defendant was not prejudiced. 

Com. v. Briggs, 5 Pick. 429 

Com. v. Briggs, 7 Pick. 177

Where an indictment alleges a second offense and the jury finds a general verdict of guilty, if no evidence was produced showing the identity of the defendant with the person formally convicted, the verdict cannot be sustained. Where an indictment containing only one count sets forth a former conviction, a nol

CRIMINAL CASES 

pros as to the matter in aggravation may be entered after a conviction on the whole indictment. 

SENTENCE 

Suspended. 

Runaldo v. Lummus, 205 Mass. 155

The right of appeal in a criminal case is only from the sentence after a conviction. The defendant must appeal then; after a suspended sentence is revoked it is then too late. Where jurisdiction is given to a court or magistrate by statute and there is no provision of appeal, the decision of the court or magistrate is final. 

Mariano v. Justices, 243 Mass. 90

The defendant was placed on probation under a suspended sentence, was subsequently surrendered. The sentence was reduced and the court ordered him to be committed. He had no right of appeal, even after there was a change in the sentence. 

Gabi's Case, 240 Mass. 465

The court can suspend a part of the sentence. The defendant was sentenced to pay a fine of $100 and to be committed for the term of one month. The fine was paid, and the execution of the sentence was suspended and the defendant placed on probation. 

Subsequently he was surrendered and the sentence was imposed. 

Now operative by General Laws, Chapter 279, Section la. 

Finer v. Com., 250 Mass. 493

A probation under a suspended sentence without a fixed date may be brought forward and the sentence imposed. 

Cherry v. Cherry, 253 Mass. 172 

A sentence temporarily suspended or delayed is not a final judgment until it becomes operative. The sentence is the final judgment in a criminal case. 

King v. Com., 246 Mass. 57

The defendant was sentenced to six months' imprisonment, and the sentence was suspended. He was placed on probation for a term of four months; when that term expired he was continued on the same probation for two successive terms; before the last term of probation he was surrendered and sentenced. Notwith-

CRIMINAL CASES 

491 standing the final term of probation was in excess of the six months' imprisonment, the sentence was legal. 

Time of starting sentence. 

Com. v. Keniston, 5 Pick. 420

The day of the commitment is to be reckoned as part of the term. 

Com. v. Hayes, 170 Mass. 16

The sentence begins on the day that sentence is passed. 

Weiner v. Wentworth, 181 Mass. 15

The defendant was sentenced to the House of Correction. A mittimus was issued. The defendant had been removed from the court and was in the prison van on his way to prison. The defendant while in the van claimed an appeal, and although the court which sentenced him was still in session, the claim was too late. 

The power of the court. 

Com. v. Rice, 216 Mass. 480

The court can recall and sentence a defendant after it has ordered the defendant to recognize for his appearance before the Superior Court. The court had declined jurisdiction. 

Smith v. Com., 178 Mass. 340

When a defendant has been sentenced for a term longer than the law allows, the sentence is reversed on a writ of error and resentenced according to law. 

Com. v. Lobel, 187 Mass. 288

The court has the power to revise its orders to correct manifest errors and to prevent injustice. 

Stalkver v. Com., 167 Mass. 11

Where there is an error in the sentence of the defendant the remedy is by a writ of error and not by a writ of habeas corpus. 

Com. v. O'Brien, 175 Mass. 37 

Com. v. Weymouth, 2 Allen, 144

If a defendant is sentenced illegally, the court has the power to modify the sentence (defendant not having been committed). 

The Superior Court is always open for the transaction of criminal business.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Foster, 122 Mass. 317

After a defendant has been sentenced and the term of the court has been adjourned without day, the court cannot amend or set aside the sentence and impose a new one instead. A defendant had been found guilty generally on an indictment containing several counts for distinct crimes, had been sentenced and committed. He cannot be brought up at a consecutive term to which term the case had not been continued and sentenced anew on another count even if the first one was void. 

Com. v. Hayes, 170 Mass. 16 

Sellers Case, 186 Mass. 301

A sentence begins in the day that sentence is passed, and after the term in which the sentence was passed has ended, the court has no power to modify it. If the sentence was unlawful, the remedy is by writ of error and not by a writ of habeas corpus. 

Com. v. Kossowan, 265 Mass. 436

Although it is the duty of the judge of the Superior Court to sentence the defendant on the conviction of the crime for which he has been found guilty, that duty is directory and the failure of the judge to do so does not impair the rights of the defendant. 

From and after. 

Kite v. Com., 11 Met. 581

A sentence to take effect from and after a former sentence, and if the former sentence is shortened or remitted, it expires, and the subsequent sentence takes effect as if the former sentence had expired by the lapse of time.

Dolan Case, 101 Mass. 219 

The sentence of a defendant is not satisfied until he actually serves it. It does not automatically run out, and a defendant who escapes before the first sentence is served must, if recaptured, serve the remainder, and a sentence which takes effect from and after the first sentence does not begin to run until the first sentence is actually satisfied. 

West's Case, 111 Mass. 443 

A prisoner under sentence who had been pardoned under the condition that if he breaks it he shall serve the residue of it, cannot under a breach of the condition be imprisoned under the first sentence, after the date when it would have expired had he not been pardoned, although his second sentence is before such date.

CRIMINAL CASES 

493 

Increasing sentence on an appeal. 

Batchelder v. Com., 109 Mass. 361

The defendant pleaded not guilty in the lower court. In the 

Superior Court he pleaded guilty. He can be sentenced when arrested upon a default warrant after his default in the Superior Court and even if he was fined in the lower court, the Superior Court can sentence him to a fine and imprisonment. 

Feeley's Case, 12 Cush. 598 

An appellate court can inflict no higher penalty for an offense than could by law be imposed by the court appealed from. 

Com. v. Gately, 203 Mass. 598

The Superior Court cannot increase the sentence on an appeal. 

A sentence longer than the law allows. 

Com. v. Morgan, 280 Mass. 392

A sentence in excess of what the law permits is not void, but only voidable on appropriate proceedings. 

Platt v. Co., 256 Mass. 539 

Koslowsky, Pet'r, 238 Mass. 532

A person convicted of the crime of fornication, the maximum sentence being three months, may be imprisoned to the Reformatory for Women and kept therein for a term of two years. 

Sylvester v. Com., 253 Mass. 244

A child may be sentenced to the Lyman School for a term longer than the maximum term prescribed by statute. 

Hard labor.

Unsoeld v. Unsoeld, 216 Mass. 594 

A sentence of imprisonment in the Massachusetts Reformatory for a term of five years is not at hard labor and no action for divorce will lie. 

Cruel and unusual punishment. 

McDonald v. Com., 173 Mass. 322

The defendant was sentenced as an habitual criminal. This is not a cruel and unusual punishment. The prohibiting of a crime can make it a crime.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Resentencing on a writ of error. 

Com. v. Murphy, 174 Mass. 369

A defendant who has been sentenced by a court having jurisdiction of the crime and of the defendant, and who has served a part of his sentence, may be resentenced if it turns out on a writ of error that the original sentence was unlawful; although the effect of the sentence will compel him to suffer solitary confinement twice, and the second sentence was for a longer period than the first, this is not double jeopardy. 

On different counts. 

Edgerton v. Com., 5 Allen, 514

When several counts in an indictment are for different crimes, a judgment and sentence on one of the counts definitely and conclusively disposes of the whole indictment. 

McNeill v. Collinson, 130 Mass. 167

One can be found guilty and sentenced on selling liquor to a minor, giving liquor to a minor, and allowing a minor to loiter on the premises. These are three different offenses and he can legally be sentenced on each one of them. 

Carlton v. Com., 5 Met. 532 

Booth v. Com., 5 Met. 535 

Murray v. Com., 13 Met. 514

Where a defendant is found guilty generally on an indictment which charges several distinct offenses, it is not necessary that separate sentences be imposed. A single sentence is legal if it does not exceed the sum of the several sentences which might be imposed. 

Josselyn v. Com., 6 Met. 236

Where one of two counts in an indictment is bad, and the defendant is found guilty and sentenced, the presumption of law is that the court awarded sentence on the good count, if the sentence is not erroneous and if it is warranted by law applicable to the offense charged in that count. 

Com. v. Harding, 283 Mass. 369

The defendant was indicted for receiving stolen goods charged in two counts. Was sentenced from 5 to 8 years. The court can impose a single sentence not exceeding the maximum sen-

CRIMINAL CASES 

495 tence. The value is immaterial. If he had been indicted on only one count, the court could not have sentenced him to more than 

5 years. 

Where no punishment is provided by statute. 

Com. v. Sherman Mfg. Co., 189 Mass. 76

General Laws, Chapter 279, Section 5, provides that if no punishment for a crime is provided by statute, the court shall impose such sentence according to the nature of the crime as conforms to the common usage and practice in this commonwealth. 

Com. v. Kelliher, 12 Allen, 480

When this decision was rendered that the prosecution must fail if the statutes provide no penalty, there was not any pro vision of the statute. Now General Laws, Chapter 279, Sec tion 5, remedies the situation. 

O'Neil v. Com., 165 Mass. 446

A person having been indicted for an assault with intent to kill is found guilty of assault only, and may be sentenced by the Superior Court for a term of three years. The lower court can sentence to not exceeding 2½ years. There is not any statute which prescribes the penalty for assault and battery so that the defendant is sentenced under Section 5 of Chapter 279 of the 

General Laws. 

Fox v. Com., 264 Mass. 51

The defendant was indicted on one indictment for conspiracy to sell liquor, on another indictment for selling and exposing liquors for sale. The defendant can be sentenced on each and the conspiracy to commit the crimes above mentioned is a separate and distinct crime. It was not error to sentence the defendant for conspiracy to do the same things for which he was sentenced for accomplishing. 

Not notifying boards before sentencing. 

Fanning v. Com., 120 Mass. 388 

The provision that before sentencing a boy to the reform school certain parties shall be notified is merely directory, and the omission to do so does not affect the validity of the sentence.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Two mittimuses, one of them legal. 

Doherty v. Munson, 127 Mass. 495

A defendant was committed to the house of correction under two mittimuses, one legal and one illegal. He was held in custody under both during the whole time of his imprisonment. He is lawfully imprisoned and the justice sentencing him, if liable at all for issuing the illegal mittimus, is liable for nominal damages only. 

Transfer of prisoner. 

Sheehan, Pet'r, 254 Mass. 342

One committed to the Shirley School may be transferred to the Massachusetts Reformatory, and this is not an abuse of judicial authority. 

Sentencing one year after. 

Dascalakis v. Com., 244 Mass. 568

The defendant may be sentenced within one year. The word is used in the permissive way. See General Laws, Chapter 278, Section 29. 

Com. v. Soderquist, 183 Mass. 199

The power to grant a new trial within one year of the original trial which is conferred on the Superior Court does not authorize the court to revoke a sentence founded upon a plea of guilty, and can make a motion for a new trial. 

Com. v. Marrelli, 266 Mass. 113

Within one year after sentence a prisoner who is committed can make a notice for a new trial. 

Com. v. Vallarelli, 273 Mass. 240

A motion filed in the Superior Court March 12, 1930, for a new trial verdict rendered March 8, 1929, and the defendant sentenced March 13, 1929-was filed within the time allowed by statute. 

Com. v. Sacco, 261 Mass. 12

The Superior Court has no jurisdiction if the motions are not filed until after sentence has been pronounced and more than a year after the verdict.

CRIMINAL CASES 

497 

Com. v. Namet, 261 Mass. 20

The court is not bound to consider a motion for a new trial filed more than a year after the end of the sitting at which the defendant was tried. This was for larceny. 

Com. v. Dascalakis, 244 Mass. 568

The word "may" in the statute is used in the permissive and not in the imperative case. 

Com. v. Phelan, 271 Mass. 21

The court has no power to grant a motion for modification of a sentence if the motion was not filed before the end of the sitting at which the sentence was imposed. 

Jail, or house of correction excessive of jail sentence. 

Lane v. Com., 161 Mass. 120

A sentence in state prison for not exceeding 5 years or not exceeding one year in jail; a person sentenced to two years in the house of correction is sentenced legally. 

Sentencing of a female for felony. 

Com. v. Moulton, 215 Mass. 525

A sentence of a female for a felony must be to the Reformatory for Women, but under Section 20 of Chapter 279 of the General Laws they also may be sentenced to a jail or house of correction for a term not exceeding 212 years. 

Sentences for larceny under $100 in value. 

Com. v. Lawless, 103 Mass. 425 

Where a complaint charges a larceny of property exceeding one hundred dollars in value the court can sentence for less than one hundred dollars, the jury having returned a verdict. The court moves that judgment and sentence be of a less value. 

Common and notorious thief. 

Evans v. Com., 3 Met. 453

One convicted of three distinct offenses at the same term shall be deemed a common and notorious thief. The condition is imperative and not conditional. 

State prison sentence may be served in jail. 

Stevens v. Com., 4 Met. 360

A prisoner who is liable to be sentenced to solitary confinement

CRIMINAL CASES 

or to hard labor in state prison three years or more, may be sentenced to the house of correction for a term not exceeding three years, although he is not liable to be sentenced to the jail for the same offense, for a term not exceeding two years; the house of correction in such case is a substitute for the state prison and not for the jail. 

Conditional sentence. 

Com. v. Briggs, 5 Met. 559 

A conditional sentence is an imprisonment. 

Practice after a reversal of judgment. 

Christian v. Com., 5 Met. 530

The Supreme Court after reversing a judgment in a criminal case cannot enter such judgment as a lower court should have done or remit the case to a lower court for judgment, and this rule applies where the sentence was to take effect at the expiration of a previous sentence. 

Void sentence, remedy for. 

Com. v. O'Neil, 6 Gray, 343

If a sentence is void, the defendant may omit to claim an appeal, or if committed be discharged on a writ, or may successfully defend on a suit on a recognizance; still he may appeal where he will be released, or a legal sentence be imposed. An appeal opens to him the whole case, as to the law, facts, and judgment. 

Larceny from the person. 

Com. v. Nolan, 5 Cush. 288

The punishment for larceny from the person does not take into consideration the amount of the property stolen. 

Lebowich v. Com., 235 Mass. 357

On several counts charging larceny from the person, a defendant can be sentenced generally, and can be tried on all the counts at the same time. 

Com. v. O'Neill, 188 Mass. 330

A defendant can be sentenced to not exceeding 212 years for attempting to commit larceny from the person. General Laws, Chapter 274, Section 6.

CRIMINAL CASES 

499 

Assault. See under Where no punishment is provided for by statute, page 495. 

When a writ of error lies. 

Cherry v. Cherry, 253 Mass. 172

A writ of error lies in a criminal case only after final judgment. 

Sennott's Case, 146 Mass. 489

Where a court has jurisdiction both of the crime and of the defendant the imposition by mistake of a sentence in excess of what the law allows is within the jurisdiction, and does not render the sentence void, but voidable on a writ of error. Probation. 

King v. Com., 246 Mass. 57

The court has the power to continue the term of the defendant's probation. Pardon. 

Com. v. Lockwood, 109 Mass. 323

The governor with the advice of the council may grant a pardon for an offense after a verdict of guilty and before the sentence, and while exceptions are pending in the Supreme Court, and the convict on waiving his exceptions and pleading the pardon is entitled to a discharge. 

Mistake in the sentence. 

Wilde v. Com., 43 Mass. 408 

An unauthorized judgment is erroneous; and it makes no difference whether the mistake is in the defendant's favor by way of an award of a sentence less than the statutory requisition or against him by way of a greater. 

Suspension of sentence or laying on file. 

Dowdican's Bail, 115 Mass. 133

An order that an indictment be laid on file is not equivalent to final judgment or to a nol pros or discontinuance. 

Sentence of joint defendants on a nuisance. 

Com. v. Hyde, Thatcher Cr. Cases, 19

Where several persons keep a common gaming house, they may be jointly or separately indicted; but, if jointly indicted, they

CRIMINAL CASES 

are to be considered as tried separately, and each is to pay the same penalty and suffer the same consequences as if it had been his sole act.

Modification of sentence. 

Com. v. Weymouth, 2 Allen, 144

A judge of the Superior Court has power to revise and increase a sentence imposed upon a prisoner during the same term of court, and before the original sentence had gone into operation, or any action has been had upon it. 

SIGNS, ADVERTISING Opinion of Justices, 232 Mass. 605 

The erection of signs near a parkway is within the law, and the Acts of 1903, Chapter 1, is unconstitutional. Only applies to advertising signs.

SLAVES 

Greenwood v. Curtis, 6 Mass. 358

Discussion as to the rights to the purchase of slaves. 

Com. v. Aves, 18 Pick. 193

A citizen of Louisiana, a slave state, who brings a slave into Massachusetts on a visit cannot take him back. 

SMALL LOAN LAWS GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 140, SEC. 86 

Dever v. Richardson, 206 Mass. 430

The police power may regulate. Acts of 1908, Chapter 605, constitutional. 

Com. v. Danziger, 176 Mass. 290

Pleadings under Chapter 497 of 1895 and amendments thereto. 

Com. v. Morris, 176 Mass. 19 

Penalty how computed per annum. 

SMOKING IN PUBLIC 

Com. v. Thompson, 12 Met. 231 

Any person having in his possession a lighted cigar or a pipe in any street, lane, or passageway applies to all open ways used

CRIMINAL CASES 

501 as such, although they have not been legally established as such. 

SNOW AND ICE

REV. ORD., CHAP. 39, SEC. 44 

Maloney v. Perreault, 275 Mass. 251

The owner of premises abutting on a public way has no duty to keep the way clear from snow, ice, or water which is upon it from natural causes, or guard against accidents by placing sawdust or other substances upon the sidewalk. 

Com. v. Watson, 97 Mass. 562

The owner of a building who has let it to two tenants, he boarding with one of them, is not liable for not shoveling off the snow. As to which tenant should do so was not decided. 

Gordon, Pet'r, 16 Pick. 504

A revised ordinance requiring the removal of snow and ice is constitutional. 

Com. v. Cutter, 156 Mass. 52 "Suffering" to remain implies an opportunity to remove and a failure to do so.

SOLICITING TO COMMIT A CRIME COMMON LAW 

Com. v. Willard, 39 Mass. 476

To commit the offense of soliciting a person to commit a crime, the crime must be of a high and aggravated nature and tending to a breach of the peace. It must be a crime mala prohibita. 

Com. v. Flagg, 135 Mass. 545 

Com. v. Harrington, 3 Pick. 26

It is an indictable crime at common law for one to counsel or solicit another person to commit a felony or other aggravated offense, although the solicitation is of no effect and the crime counseled is not in fact committed.

SPECULATOR 

Opinion of the Justices, 247 Mass. 583

The act providing an additional charge of fifty cents on tickets of admission by a ticket speculator is constitutional.

CRIMINAL CASES 

In relation to the above see the opinion of Zottoli, J., relating to Section 185D of Chapter 140 of the General Laws, who declared the act was unconstitutional, that the speculator could charge any additional fee over and above the box office price that he desired.

General Laws, Section 185D, provides: "No Licensee under 

Section one hundred and eighty-five shall resell any ticket or other evidence of right of entry to any theatrical exhibition, public show or public amusement or exhibition of any description at a price in excess of fifty cents in advance of the price printed on the face of such ticket or other evidence of right of entry as the purchase price thereof." (Penalty, fine not more than $500. 

Section 185D.)

This Act was originally enacted in 1924 (June 5). (Acts 1924, Chapter 497.)

Before passing this Act the General Court had doubts as to the constitutionality of the Act and requested an opinion of our Supreme Judicial Court regarding its constitutionality. 

On April 23, 1924, the Justices returned an answer which concludes: "We are of opinion that a limitation of additional prices on resale to a sum not exceeding fifty cents on each ticket cannot be pronounced unreasonable." A statute similar in its substantial features to the one here proposed has recently been held constitutional in People v. Weller, 237 N. Y. 316. 

But in October, 1926, the United States Supreme Court held the New York Statute above referred to unconstitutional because it deprived the defendant of its liberty and property without due process of law, in violation of the 14th Amendment to the Constitution of the United States, Tyson and Bro. v. Banton, 273 

U. S. 418.

The same doctrine was again affirmed in the United States (S. J. C.) in the case of Rebnick v. McBride, 277 U. S. 350, 357, the Court stating: "In the case of Tyson and Brothers Theatre

Ticket Offices, case 273 U. S. 418, we declared unconstitutional an Act of the New York Legislature which sought to fix the price at which theater tickets should be sold by a ticket broker, and it is not easy to see how, without disregarding that decision, price-fixing legislation in respect to other brokers of like character can be upheld." (The Court here holding that price fixing of Employment Agency fees was unconstitutional.) 

In Williams v. Standard Oil Co., 278 U. S. 234, 240 (1928) the Court said: It is settled by recent decisions of this Court that a State Legislature is without constitutional power to fix

CRIMINAL CASES 

503 prices at which commodities may be sold, services rendered, or property used unless the business or property involved is "affected with a public interest." "In order to be affected with a public interest, it (business or property) must be such or be so employed as to justify the conclusion that it has been devoted to a public use and its use thereby in effect granted to the public." "It does not mean that a business is affected with a public interest merely because it is large or because the public are warranted in having a feeling of concern in respect of its maintenance. 

We are concerned with the character of the business not with its size or extent to which the commodity is used." 

Com. v. Sorensky, 269 Mass. 460

A single resale of a ticket to a wrestling match warranted a finding that the defendant was engaged in the business of reselling tickets. STABLE 

City of Newton v. Joyce, 166 Mass. 83

Prohibitive occupation of the use of a bed for more than four horses, unless licensed by the board of health, is an exercise of police power and there is no provision for appeal or compensation.

STATUTES AND STATUTORY CONSTRUCTION 

Criminal statutes. 

Com. v. Alexander, 185 Mass. 551

Criminal statutes are to be construed strictly and cannot be enlarged by implication. 

Wilson v. Head, 184 Mass. 515

General Laws, Chapter 4, Section 6, Clause 2, which applies to the repeal, etc., applies only to penal statutes. 

Harvey v. Inhab. of Easton, 189 Mass. 505

A penal statute is not to be extended by implication. 

Com. v. Bennett, 108 Mass. 27

An act which was committed under a previous statute which was repealed was in force between the time when the governor signed the bill and the time that it went into effect.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Pentz, 247 Mass. 500 

A statute which creates a crime must be definite in specifying the conduct which is commanded or prohibited. 

Com. v. Freelove, 150 Mass. 66

The Bill of Rights, Article 12, could not authorize the omissions of allegations necessary to describe a specific crime. The omission of "against the peace" does not void a complaint. 

Com. v. Drohan, 210 Mass. 445 

Com. v. Hana, 195 Mass. 262

The repeal of a statute does not affect the court sentencing a defendant, if at the time the crime was committed the law was in force. 

Com. v. Desmond, 123 Mass. 407

The repeal of an act shall not affect any prosecution pending at the time of the repeal for an offense committed under the act repealed. 

Com. v. Wilkins, 121 Mass. 356

A city ordinance which provides a penalty of more than $20 is void. 

Com. v. Pentz, 247 Mass. 500

Statutes creating crimes must be definite. 

Com. v. Bennett, 108 Mass. 27 

Com. v. Marshall, 11 Pick. 350

There can be no legal conviction of a crime unless the act is contrary to the law at the time it was committed, nor can there be any judgment unless the law making the act unlawful is in force at the time of the indictment and judgment. 

Libbey v. N. H. Ry., 273 Mass. 522

A penal statute must be construed strictly and not extended by equity or by the probable or supposed intention of the legislature as derived from doubtful words; but in order to charge a party with a penalty he must be brought within its operation as manifested by the express words or necessary implication. 

A railroad car which operates by gasoline could not come within the statute of cars operated by steam. 

Com. v. Wyman, 12 Cush. 237

An act mitigating the punishment for an offense but not changing the offense is not ex post facto, it is an act of clemency.

CRIMINAL CASES 

505 

Com. v. Loring, 8 Pick. 370

Penal statutes, though they are to be construed strictly, are yet to receive such a construction as will conform to the manifest intention of the legislature and avoid an absurdity. 

Title of a statute. 

Com. v. Flax, 259 Mass. 117

The title of an act, ordinance, or regulation generally does not control the words of the enactment, unless they are doubtful or ambiguous. 

Loraine Steel Co. v. Briston Ry., 187 Mass. 500

The use of a word in the title of a statute cannot be held to control or enlarge the words of the statute, unless they are of doubtful meaning and ambiguous. 

Repeal of statutes. 

Com. v. Newhall, 205 Mass. 344

A statute which repeals all existing laws, ordinances, and park regulations is valid. 

Doyle v. Kirby, 184 Mass. 409

The repeal of a statute which seems to have intended to cover the whole subject to which it relates, impliedly repeals all existing statutes touching the subject and supersedes the common law. 

Johnson v. Mayor of Quincy, 198 Mass. 411

A statute is not held to have been repealed by implication unless the new legislation is inconsistent with the old. 

Com. v. Kendall, 144 Mass. 357

The repealing of a statute by a subsequent act where part of a prior act is incorporated in a subsequent act, is the same thing as if the words of the first act were incorporated in the second act.

Falmouth v. Gen. Adv. Co., 264 Mass. 85 

A statute is not to be deemed to supersede a former statute in whole or in part in the absence of express words or a clear implication. 

Com. v. Kimball, 21 Pick. 373

Those parts of the original statute which are inconsistent with the amended, and those only, are repealed by implication.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Brooks v. Fitchburg Rd., 200 Mass. 8

Repeal statutes which are alleged to be inconsistent with each other in whole or in part must be construed so as to give reasonable effect to all unless some positive repugnance appears. 

Bunnell v. Hixon, 205 Mass. 468 

Repeal of a repealing statute. 

Com. v. Kelliher, 12 Allen, 480

Whenever a statute is passed which embraces all the provisions of previous statutes on the same subject, the new statute operates as a repeal of all antecedent enactments. 

Com. v. Bradley, 16 Gray, 241

An indictment was found for assault and battery on May 20, 1860. On June 1, 1860, all laws were re-enacted. On appeal the defendant could be convicted, for the law was in force at the time the crime was committed. 

Com. v. Marshall, 11 Pick. 350

When the repeal of an existing statute revives the existing law, such revival takes effect only from the time of such appeal. 

Com. v. Churchill, 2 Met. 118

The simple repeal of a repealing statute revives the original statute. 

Saving clause. 

Com. v. Kimball, 21 Pick. 373

Where there is no saving clause in the statute, judgment must be arrested in regard to the prosecutions then pending. 

Com. v. McDonough, 13 Allen, 581

If a law is repealed by a subsequent one without a saving clause, one who committed a crime before the enactment cannot be punished. In the above case there was a sentence provided by both laws. 

Com. v. Bennett, 108 Mass. 30

Nothing contained in the statute shall affect any penalty or forfeiture already incurred under the provisions of law in force prior to the passage of this act. Saves from the operation of the

CRIMINAL CASES 

507 statute any penalty incurred before it took effect, although after it was approved by the governor. 

Wilson v. Head, 184 Mass. 515

The provisions of the statute which provides that the repeal of an act shall not affect any prosecution, suit, or proceeding pending at the time of the repeal for an offense committed, or for the recovery of a forfeiture or a penalty, etc., apply to statutes which are strictly penal. 

Class legislation. 

Opinion of Justices, 207 Mass. 601 

A statute which makes it a criminal offense to admit to a Chinese restaurant women under the age of twenty-one years or to serve them food therein is unconstitutional. 

Simonds v. Simonds, 103 Mass. 572

It can never be within the bounds of legitimate legislation to enact a special law, or pass a resolve, dispensing with the general law in a particular case, and granting a privilege and indulgence to one man by way of exemption from the operation and effect of such general law, leaving all other persons under its operation. 

Granting cities the right to enact laws. 

Com. v. Davis, 162 Mass. 510

The legislature in granting cities the right to make rules and regulations for the parks is constitutional. 

Com. v. Colton, 8 Gray, 488

It is within the power of the legislature to make police regulations as to hours and modes of occupying places of amusement, so as to make their use consistent with the peace of the community. 

Com. v. Worcester, 3 Pick. 462

It is no objection that a statute may be confined to one city alone. 

Common law. 

Pen Ins. Co. v. Hunt, 237 Mass. 241

The statutes of Massachusetts have superseded the rules of common law wherever they are declaratory to it.

CRIMINAL CASES Com. v. Rumford Chem. Works, 16 Gray, 231 

It is never to be presumed that the legislature intended to make any innovations upon the common law further than is absolutely required upon a just interpretation of the provisions of its positive enactments. A statute is not to be construed as a repeal of the common law unless the intent to alter it is clearly expressed.

Laws of other states. 

Ely v. James, 123 Mass. 36

The laws of other states must be proved, but if the evidence admitted consists of a judicial opinion, its construction is for the court. 

Murphy v. Collins, 121 Mass. 6

There is no presumption that the statutory law of another state is the same as that of the Commonwealth of Massachusetts. 

Com. v. Kenney, 120 Mass. 387

A law of a foreign country is a matter of fact which our courts cannot take judicial notice of without proof; it must be proved as a matter of fact. 

Universal Fixture Corp. v. Mark, 247 Mass. 85

When the law of a foreign state is not in evidence, the case is governed by the common law. 

Words and phrases. 

Com. v. Greenwood, 205 Mass. 124

Words and phrases shall be construed according to the common usage, but for technical words see Chapter 4, Section 6, Clause 2, of the General Laws. 

Burrage v. County of Bristol, 210 Mass. 299

Evidence to explain the peculiar meaning of a word as used in a written instrument never has been applied to the language of the statutes. 

Williams v. O. C. Ry., 193 Mass. 305

A limiting or qualifying word must be confined to the last antecedent unless the subject matter requires a different construction.

CRIMINAL CASES 

509 

Com. v. Kelley, 177 Mass. 221 

Com. v. Grant, 210 Mass. 458

Punctuation may be disregarded in construing a statute; it may be resorted to as an aid in construing, when it tends to throw light on the matter. 

Shall take effect. 

Com. v. Brooks, 109 Mass. 355

An ordinance which reads "shall take effect" and be in force from and after the time therein respectively limited takes effect, that is, is in force, immediately upon its passage. 

Part valid, part invalid. 

Greene v. Mayor of Fitchburg, 219 Mass. 121

Where the greater part of an ordinance attempted to be passed by a city is invalid and the rest of the ordinance is so connected with the void part as to constitute with it an entirety, from which it is not separable, the whole is void. 

Com. v. Petranich, 183 Mass. 217

A part of a statute which is unconstitutional does not affect the rest of the statute. 

Previous statute judicially construed. 

King v. Thissell, 222 Mass. 140

When the legislature in enacting a statute adopts the language of a previous statute that has received a judicial construction, the construction is deemed to have been adopted also, and is applied to the latter statute. 

When a statute is not clear. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 172 Mass. 311

Where a statute is not precise or clear, such construction will be adopted as shall appear most reasonable and best suited to accomplish the object of the statute, and where any peculiar construction would lead to an absurd consequence. 

Interpretation of statute. 

Brown v. Robinson, 275 Mass. 55 

Com. v. Welosky, 276 Mass. 398

In the interpretation of a statute its words are the main source for the ascertainment of a legislative purpose. In con-

CRIMINAL CASES 

sidering the words of the statute the title of a legislative act may be taken into consideration and count in its interpretation. 

Com. v. Gallo, 275 Mass. 320

Persons charged with crime have no vested rights in merely procedural matters not vitally connected with the real defense. 

Burrage v. Bristol County, 210 Mass. 299

Where the language of a statute is of doubtful import a construction put upon it for many years, which was not amended, by officers charged with its supervision as to public duties, is strong evidence of its meaning. 

Old Colony Trust Co. v. Com., 220 Mass. 409

Where a provision of a statute passed previously is made applicable to a new subject of legislation, not contemplated when the earlier statute was passed, such an interpretation of the application must be adopted as will work out practical results. 

Com. v. Crane, 216 Mass. 356

If by a rational interpretation a legitimate purpose can be attributed to a statute which otherwise might be unconstitutional, such legitimate purpose must be assumed to have been the intent of the legislature. 

Franks v. Eidenberg, 185 Mass. 49

Where there is a substantial doubt as to the meaning of the language used in the compilation of a statute, the statute in question as it previously existed, especially when taken with a note of the commissioners indicating an intention not to make any change, furnishes a valuable guide to the construction of the statute in the new form. 

Pratt v. St. Com., 139 Mass. at 563

If the language of a section of the General Laws is ambiguous, or susceptible of two constructions, the court in determining its meaning will consider the language of the statute of which it is a revision. 

Com. v. Int. Liq., 105 Mass. 178

Where one part of a statute will admit of two constructions, the one consistent with, and the other repugnant to, other parts of the same statute, the construction which is consistent is to be adopted.

CRIMINAL CASES 

511 

Com. v. Hitchings, 5 Gray, 482 

Com. v. Rock, 10 Gray, 4

Where one part of a statute is unconstitutional, this will not authorize the court to declare the remainder void unless all the provisions are connected in the subject matter, depending on each other together for the same purpose, or otherwise so connected in meaning that it cannot be presumed that the legislature would have passed one without the other. 

Interpretation after codification. 

Com. v. N. Y. C. Ry., 206 Mass. 417 

Interpretation of statutes after codification. Retroactive.

City of Haverhill v. City of Marlboro, 187 Mass. 150 

A statute is not to be construed as retroactive unless an intention that it should affect past transactions is expressed in its performance. 

Previous conviction. 

Com. v. Harrington, 130 Mass. 35

A clause of a statute which provided that it shall not be necessary in a complaint under it to allege previous convictions is imperative and void as being contrary to the provisions of the Declaration of the Bill of Rights. 

Counting Sundays. 

Stevenson v. Donnolly, 221 Mass. 161

Where a statute fixes the limitations of time within which a particular act may or may not be done, if the time is less than one week, Sundays are excluded. 

Parts omitted. 

Com. v. Taylor, 132 Mass. 261

Where a statute is revised, or one act framed from another, if some parts are omitted, they are not to be revived by construction but are to be considered as annulled. 

Conferring power on lower tribunals. 

Com. v. Horregan, 127 Mass. 450

An act which confers on lower tribunals the right to punish by infamous punishment is unconstitutional. The Statutes of

CRIMINAL CASES 

1870, Chapter 359, Section 11, conferred power on juvenile police and municipal courts to try without jury and without indictment boys charged with crimes punishable in the state prison. 

See General Laws, Chapter 218, Section 26. 

Civil penalty. 

Allen v. Clark, 190 Mass. 556

A statute which provides a civil liability for failure to comply with its requirements partakes of the nature of a penal statute and should be construed strictly. 

Crimes committed before statute, indictment after. 

Com. v. Homer, 153 Mass. 343

A crime committed before the enactment of a statute, indictment found after. All statutes are to be considered prospective and are not to be held to prejudice or affect the past transactions of the subject, unless such intention is clearly and unequivocally expressed. 

Lawful before, unlawful afterwards. 

Com. v. Sherman Mfg. Co., 189 Mass. 76

When a valid statute prohibits acts lawful before its passage, a contract to do the prohibited act made before the passage of the statute is no defense to an indictment for the violation of the statute. 

New legislation. 

Bk. Com. v. Prudential Tr. Co., 240 Mass. 244

When legislation covers the entire field, previous provisions of either common or statutory law in conflict therewith become no longer operative.

Construction of a re-enactment. 

Lowe v. Blanchard, 116 Mass. 272

The re-enactment without a substantial alteration of a statute already construed must receive the same construction as that statute.

Process under an unconstitutional statute. 

Kelley v. Bemis, 4 Gray, 83 A justice who issues process under an unconstitutional statute is liable to damages to the person arrested thereon.

CRIMINAL CASES 

513 Legislature cannot pass law to aid individual. 

Holden v. James, 11 Mass. 396

The legislature has no authority under the constitution to suspend the operation of a general law in favor of an individual. 

States cannot enact laws in opposition to Congress. 

Com. v. Knox, 6 Mass. 76

The states cannot pass statutes prohibitory to the execution of powers authorized by Congress.

Initiative and referendum. 

Com. v. Higgins, 277 Mass. 191

Legislation becoming operative under the initiative must conform to the constitution to the same extent as statutes enacted by the General Court. 

Statute of limitations. 

Com. v. Ross, 248 Mass. 15

In a prosecution for polygamy, statute of limitations does not run.

STOLEN PROPERTY Restoration of. See Gen. Laws, Chap. 266, Sec. 48. 

Com. v. McDonald, 187 Mass. 581

The court having found one A to be the owner of stolen goods, the same was ordered to be restored to the owner. 

Banks v. Farwell, 21 Pick. 156

Under a warrant in usual form on a complaint for larceny the officer is authorized to break and enter the shop of the person accused and seize the chattel alleged to have been stolen. 

Com. v. Boudrie, 4 Gray, 418

The right of an owner of stolen property to a summary restitution thereof is limited to the articles stolen, and does not extend to the money into which the same was exchanged by the thief. 

King v. Ham, 6 Allen, 298 

An officer who retains a promissory note two years after conviction of the thief who stole it, and the maker in the meantime becomes bankrupt, is liable on the note.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Briggs v. Shepard Mfg. Co., 217 Mass. 446

It is no part of the search warrant proceedings to try the person in whose possession the stolen goods were found. A conviction of larceny cannot be legally founded upon a search warrant. If the goods are found in the place described, an additional complaint should be made directly charging the suspected person with feloniously taking them. 

Search warrant. 

Sandford v. Nichols, 13 Mass. 286

A warrant issued by a competent tribunal will protect the serving officer although founded on insufficient evidence. A warrant directing the search of a dwelling house authorizes the search of the shop of the defendant if it is in the same place. 

The warrant must describe the goods with a degree of certainty and not generally. A warrant to search the house of a company will not warrant the search of the house of a member of that company. 

Banks v. Farwell, 38 Mass. 156

Under a warrant duly issued for larceny (an arrest warrant) the officer can search the house of the defendant and seize chattels even to the extent of breaking in. 

Com. v. Dana, 43 Mass. 329

A search warrant may refer to an annexed complaint for a description of the goods to be searched for. 

Com. v. Tucker, 189 Mass. 457

A warrant directs the officer to take all the goods. The occupant of the house did not desire to see the search warrant but invited the officers in such search being made under the invitation and not under the warrant. This is not an abuse of legal process. 

Briggs v. Shepard Mfg. Co., 217 Mass. 446

Procedure as to stolen property. Some goods were found by virtue of the warrant, but no complaint was ever instituted against any defendant. The property was ordered to be returned. 

Stone v. Dana, 5 Met. 98

A warrant directs the officer to take all the goods and arrest the person in whose possession the same are found. It is proper to insert the name of the person in whose building the of-

CRIMINAL CASES 

515 ficer suspects the goods are concealed and to arrest him if goods are found in his possession. 

Dwinnells v. Boynton, 3 Allen, 310 

Sandford v. Nichols, 13 Mass. 286

A warrant for stolen property issued by a competent tribunal will protect an officer who acts under it, although founded on an insufficient complaint. The warrant called for the search of a dwelling house and the officer searched the shop of This fact did not make the officer a trespasser ab initio. 

Homrich v. Robinson, 221 Mass. 308

A suit in equity may be brought to recover stolen property in the hands of the law taken from the thief when he was arrested for another crime, and when the officer refused to expose it, so as to be replevined. 

Com. v. McDonald, 187 Mass. 581

Property in the hands of a thief must be restored to the owner. See Section 48 of Chapter 266 of the General Laws. 

STREET COMMISSIONERS 

Com. v. Davis, 140 Mass. 485

The provisions of law that ordinances and bylaws must be published is directory only and there is no provision of law that they shall not take effect until such publication. It is not a condition precedent to the validity of the ordinance. 

Com. v. Fenton, 139 Mass. 195

The defendant stopped on a street and just before the twenty minutes had expired (the legal limit) he moved along the street and remained a further time of nine minutes. Not in the same spot but was guilty. 

Com. v. Rowe, 141 Mass. 79

A regulation that a vehicle shall not stop more than twenty minutes in a street means obviously a continued stop of twenty minutes. 

Com. v. Flax, 259 Mass. 117

The commonwealth need not prove beyond a reasonable doubt that the officer was acting in good faith, or that it was necessary to move the vehicle to avoid congestion or to promote

CRIMINAL CASES 

safety or convenience. The officer arrested the defendant at the time of committing the offense without a warrant. 

Com. v. Derby, 162 Mass. 183

A stop is more than a halt for an instant under an exigency which would require or justify halting. 

Com. v. Allen, 203 Mass. 529

Publication of the traffic rules and regulations upheld. 

Com. v. Gile, 217 Mass. 18

The automobile laws do not repeal the traffic laws and do not conflict with the law of the road. General Laws, Chapter 88, Section 4, or Chapter 90, Section 17, do not affect the traffic regulations. The automobile law is constitutional. 

Lynch v. B. E. Ry., 224 Mass. 93

Section 3 of the traffic regulations giving fire apparatus the right of way does not apply to instructing firemen in the operation of a fire truck.

STREET RAILWAY. -OBSTRUCTING GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 161, SEC. 94 

Com. v. Temple, 14 Gray, 69

Driving a team on a railroad track for a distance of several hundred feet is guilty of willfully and maliciously obstructing the railway. Intent is not necessary.

STRIKES 

De Minico v. Craig, 207 Mass. 593

Whether a purpose for which a labor strike is instituted is or is not a legal justification for the strike is a question of law for the court. 

Steinert v. Tagen, 207 Mass. 394

The members of a labor union, four months after a strike has ended, cannot lawfully drive about the streets a wagon bearing placards stating that the strikers are on a strike for hours and wages. 

Com. v. Libbey, 216 Mass. 356

The laws against advertising for help during a strike and not stating the fact that a strike exists is constitutional. The publishing in one paper is sufficient.

CRIMINAL CASES 

517 

SUICIDE 

Com. v. Mink, 123 Mass. 422

In this commonwealth suicide is deemed criminal as malum in se, and although an attempt to commit suicide is not punishable, yet a person who in attempting to commit suicide accidentally kills another who is trying to prevent it is guilty of manslaughter. 

Com. v. Bowen, 13 Mass. 356

One counsels another to commit suicide. The adviser is guilty of murder as a principal. 

Com. v. Dennis, 105 Mass. 162

An attempt to commit suicide is not indictable in this commonwealth.

THREATENING TO DO BODILY HARM GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 275 

Com. v. Mosby, 163 Mass. 291

To arrest a person on a civil process cannot be held a threat to injure the person of another. 

Com. v. Rollo, 203 Mass. 354

Threats made to the mother of a girl who was assaulted, by the wife of the defendant in the absence of the defendant, are admissible. 

Com. v. White, 162 Mass. 403

Letters written six months previous are admissible. 

Com. v. Holmes, 157 Mass. 233

Threats made by a husband to his wife extending over a period of nine years up to the time of the indictment are not too remote, and are admissible to show the course of conduct. 

TRADE MARKS GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 110 

Com. v. Anselvich, 186 Mass. 376

The state courts have jurisdiction where the trade mark is registered under the United States laws.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Batchelder v. Batchelder, 220 Mass. 42 

The word "person" includes a corporation.

TRADING STAMPS GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 271, SEC. 30 

Com. v. Sisson, 178 Mass. 578

Giving trading stamps with goods to enable the purchaser to obtain goods in return therefor in another store is not a violation of Section 29 of Chapter 271 of the General Laws, as there is no gambling element.

O'Keeffe v. City of Somerville, 190 Mass. 110 

Attempting to impose an excise tax on the trading stamp business is unconstitutional and void, the right to transact business in this manner not being a commodity within the meaning of the word "commodities" and the restriction of this business not being a proper exercise of police power. 

Opinion of Justices, 208 Mass. 607 

A law that gift enterprises are unlawful is void. 

Com. v. Emerson, 165 Mass. 146

A law that forbids the giving away of one article conditioning on the purchase of another, gift enterprise so called, is illegal. 

TRESPASS GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 266, SEC. 120 

Com. v. Haley, 4 Allen, 318 

Meader v. Stone, 7 Met. 147

A landlord having the right of immediate possession may take it without legal process if he can do so peaceably, but he has no right to forcibly remove his tenant's goods or commit an assault on him while so doing. The landlord took off the doors and windows where there was sickness, but he could not eject the tenant. by actual force.

Podespik v. Worcester Ry., 216 Mass. 213 

One who boards a car and refuses to pay his fare is a trespasser and has no rights as a passenger.

CRIMINAL CASES 

519 

Richmond v. Fiske, 160 Mass. 34

One who enters the outer door by permission of the occupant to deliver milk, does not become a trespasser by entering the latter's sleeping room, against previous commands, for the purpose of presenting a bill. 

Smith v. Aircraft Co., 270 Mass. 511

An invasion of the air space above the land without contact with its surface constitutes a trespasser. There was no damage done and no amount can be recovered, although perhaps the market value of the land may be depreciated. The owner has no remedy for compensation. 

Clark v. Kelliher, 107 Mass. 406

One has no right to kill hens that are trespassing upon his property, even if he has notified the owner that he will do so if they are not restrained. 

Com. v. White, 11 Cush. 483

The defendant took a horse and wagon from a stable and drove to a nearby town. While on his way he changed horses. He then drove to another county, where he sold them. Although he took the property and intended to return it, he can be convicted of larceny in the county where he committed the trespass. 

Discussion between larceny and trespass. 

Gray v. Parke, 162 Mass. 582

A guardian of an insane ward can bar the admittance of anyone, and one who tries to do so is guilty of trespass. 

Fitzgerald v. Lewis, 164 Mass. 495

The overseers of poor of a city in control of the poor farm have authority to post signs on the land forbidding the trespass. A notice need not be signed. Need not prove that the trespasser saw the notice. 

Brown v. Perkins, 83 Mass. 89

An entry into the building of another without license, express or implied, is trespass. 

Taylor v. Townsend, 8 Mass. 411

Where a tenancy is of a higher character than a tenancy at will the landlord cannot maintain trespass.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Shattuck, 4 Cush. 141

A merely unlawful entry upon the land of another, with technical force and arms but accompanied by no acts beyond a simple trespasser, is not an indictable offense. 

TRIAL 

See under Practice.

UNNATURAL ACT GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 272, SEC. 35 

Com. v. Dill, 160 Mass. 536

An indictment alleging that the defendant did commit unlawfully an unnatural and lascivious act with one A sufficiently alleges the crime. 

Com. v. Delano, 197 Mass. 166

A person who commits an unnatural act with another person is within the statute. Copulation with a person is the natural act of coition and does not fall within the statute. 

UNITED STATES 

Com. v. Closson, 229 Mass. 329

A mail wagon and carrier are amenable to the laws of this commonwealth; the facilities afforded for the transportation of mails confer no extraordinary rights on mail carriers to use the ways as they please, nor do they necessarily or impliedly do away with the powers of supervision and control inherent in the state. 

Com. v. Knox, 6 Mass. 76 Carrying mail on the Lord's day is not a violation; the United 

States has the right to contract and make laws and a state cannot pass laws that conflict. 

Tepper v. B. E. Ry., 192 Mass. 46

A trolley car carrying the mail has no more right to the exclusive right of the street than any other car. It cannot violate the law; all the protection that it is entitled to, is that no one shall hinder the passage of it.

CRIMINAL CASES 

521 

Com. v. Breakwater Co., 214 Mass. 10

A vessel licensed and registered under the federal laws is not necessarily removed from the police power of the state. 

VACCINATION GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 111, SEC. 181 

Com. v. Pear, 183 Mass. 242 

Com. v. Green, 268 Mass. 585

General Laws, Chapter 111, Section 181, as to compulsory vaccination is constitutional. Scruples and religious belief are no defense. 

Spoffard v. Carleton, 238 Mass. 528

A regulation that a pupil must be examined every two months for not being vaccinated under a school regulation is not discriminatory.

VAGABOND 

GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 272, SEC. 68 

Com. v. Ellis, 207 Mass. 572 

An allegation in a complaint that on the ... ... day of .. and on divers other days and times, etc., add nothing to the complaint and can be stricken out as surplusage. The vagabond law is constitutional. The time alleged is definite. 

VERDICT 

General verdict. 

Com. v. Storti, 177 Mass. 339

An indictment charged a murder in two counts; one count admittedly is bad, the other count is good; a general verdict can be rendered. 

Com. v. Tolman, 149 Mass. 229

When all the counts in a complaint are different descriptions of the same offense, only one penalty is incurred. 

Com. v. Nichols, 134 Mass. 531

If one count is good and one count is bad, there can be a general verdict entered on any sufficient count.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. B. & M. Ry., 133 Mass. 383

The rule where the same offense is charged in different counts is that the whole indictment may be submitted to the jury with instructions that if they find the defendant guilty upon any count they are to return a general verdict of guilty. This is not applicable in the case where one count is bad and the evidence applicable to such count is submitted to the jury with the rest against the objection of the defendant. 

Com. v. Fitchburg Ry., 120 Mass. 372

Where an indictment charges a single crime in several counts as committed in different ways, inconsistent with each other, a general verdict should be returned upon the whole indictment, as for a single crime; or a verdict of guilty on the count proved and not guilty on the others; it is a mistrial to allow the jury to return a verdict of guilty on each count, and if such a verdict is returned the commonwealth is not entitled to enter a nol pros to all the counts but one, and retain the verdict as to that one. 

Com. v. Herty, 109 Mass. 348 

First count charged the murder by kicks and blows; second count charged the murder by means unknown. The jury returned a verdict of murder in the second degree. The verdict was a general verdict and covers the charges in the indictment. 

Com. v. Chisholm, 103 Mass. 213

On a trial on an indictment charging three separate crimes the jury must consider each count by itself, and a general verdict without this being done entitles the defendant to a new trial. 

Edgerton v. Com., 5 Allen, 514

Three crimes charged in one indictment. A verdict of guilty on the second, a verdict of not guilty on the first, and no verdict on the third. He was sentenced on the second count. The judgment was good. They being different descriptions of the same offense, there can be only one judgment. There may be a verdict on all three but only one sentence on the indictment. 

Lesser verdict than indicted on. 

Com. v. Crowley, 167 Mass. 434 

Com. v. Crowley, 168 Mass. 222

A verdict was rendered for assault and battery with intent to commit larceny. This cannot be supported to its full extent but

CRIMINAL CASES 

523 is not wholly invalid, as the defendant may be sentenced for assault and battery. 

Com. v. Cooper, 15 Mass. 187

The defendant was charged with the crime of rape. The jury could bring in a verdict of a lesser crime as assault with intent to rape. 

Com. v. Creadon, 162 Mass. 466

Indictment charges assault with intent to rape. The jury can render a verdict of rape. 

Com. v. McCarthy, 165 Mass. 37 

Com. v. Drum, 19 Pick. 479

Offense is assault and battery with intent to ravish. The jury can render a verdict of assault and battery, whether or not the intent to ravish is well alleged or not. 

Com. v. Salah, 253 Mass. 549

The defendant was charged with breaking and entering. The evidence disclosed that the stolen property was in the possession of the defendant. The jury can render a verdict of larceny of the property. 

Com. v. Lynes, 142 Mass. 577

The defendant was charged with incest. The court charged that carnal knowledge and penetration were necessary in order to convict the defendant, and that an unsuccessful attempt to accomplish this did not constitute the crime charged. 

Com. v. Blaney, 133 Mass. 571 

A complaint for assault and battery with intent to maim, etc., was drawn without pleading the aggravation but charged a formal complaint for assault and battery. The defendant could not plead specially as to assault and battery, but could on the question of evidence object to any sentence except for assault and battery. 

Com. v. O'Brien, 119 Mass. 342 

Com. v. Lang, 10 Gray, 11

An indictment charged an assault and battery with a dangerous weapon, to wit, a knife, with intent to kill. The jury returned a verdict of guilty of assault and battery with a knife but without the intent.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. McGrath, 115 Mass. 150

The indictment was for assault with intent to maim and disfigure. Verdict was rendered for assault without the intent. The defendant can be sentenced for a simple assault. 

Com. v. Dean, 109 Mass. 349 

The defendant was charged with rape. The district attorney nol prossed the allegation as to rape. The defendant was sentenced for assault. 

Com. v. Stoddard, 9 Allen, 280

Indictment alleged leaving a child in the street exposed to the elements. Jury rendered a verdict for assault. 

Com. v. O'Connell, 12 Allen, 451

A complaint charged the larceny of $150. The jury can render a verdict of the larceny of $45. It is competent to convict the defendant of stealing a part of the whole sum charged. 

Com. v. Lang, 10 Gray, 11

The defendant was charged with assault with intent to murder. Jury returned a verdict of assault and battery without the intent. The court struck out the battery. Verdict good. 

Com. v. Murphy, 2 Allen, 163

Neither at common law nor under the statutes could a verdict be properly received or recorded convicting a defendant of any lesser offense than the felony set forth in the indictment, unless such minor offense was substantially charged by the residue of the indictment, after striking out that portion of which the defendant was acquitted. 

Com. v. Duffy, 11 Cush. 145

Judgment will not be arrested for larceny of sundry bank bills of the aggregate value of $367, merely because the verdict was "guilty of stealing sundry bank bills of $317 and not guilty of the residue." 

Com. v. Burke, 14 Gray, 100 "Matters of aggravation" is not a descriptive averment when alleged affirmatively under an indictment charging an assault with aggravation. The defendant may be convicted of the substantive crime without the aggravation.

CRIMINAL CASES 

525 

Com. v. Goodhue, 2 Met. 193

A defendant indicted for rape on his daughter may be convicted of incest, if the jury finds the criminal connection but that it was not by force and against her will. 

Com. v. Prescott, 153 Mass. 396

A complaint charged the defendant with illegally taking 1000 smelts. This is but one offense. If the lower court only found the defendant guilty of taking 330 smelts, the verdict did not amount to an acquittal of the remainder. 

Different counts. 

Com. v. Hackett, 170 Mass. 194

Jury returned a verdict as to certain counts. Judgment was entered, the other counts treated as an acquittal or a discontinuance. 

Com. v. Dow, 217 Mass. 473

Different descriptions of the same offense. Can make a finding of acquittal and conviction as to the same. 

Com. v. Haskins, 128 Mass. 60

A jury cannot return a verdict of guilty on both counts of an indictment charging larceny and receiving stolen goods, and a nol pros of the second count does not cure the defect. 

Wingersky v. E. E. Gray Co., 254 Mass. 198

A finding of guilty of theft and receiving is inconsistent. 

Com. v. Foster, 122 Mass. 317

When a verdict of guilty has been rendered on one count, the defendant may be lawfully sentenced thereon, although no verdict has been rendered on the other count. 

Com. v. Mead, 10 Allen, 398

The first count charged a nuisance, the second count selling. 

The date of the selling was for a sale during the time embraced in the first count. The defendant pleaded guilty to the selling and not guilty to the nuisance. The jury could render a verdict of guilty as to the nuisance. Cured by the district attorney nol prossing the second count, as there had not been any judgment, only a verdict.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Packard, 5 Gray, 101

A misjoinder of counts is cured by a verdict for the defendant on the count improperly joined. Variance.

Com. v. Campopiano, 254 Mass. 560 

A verdict of not guilty by reason of variance is not a verdict on the merits and is not a bar to a subsequent prosecution on the same evidence. A record that the defendant's acquittal was by reason of variance cannot be contradicted. 

Com. v. Cabot, 241 Mass. 131 

"Not guilty on the grounds of variance" is a proper record when the judge ruled as a matter of law. The evidence did not support the offense charged. 

Joinder of defendants. 

Com. v. Richardson, 142 Mass. 71

A person who paddles a boat in which another person is fishing in violation of the law may be convicted of illegally fishing as a participant in the offense. 

Com. v. Cook, 12 Allen, 542

If two defendants are joined in one indictment for selling liquor, and single sales are relied on for their conviction, and only one of them is convicted, this is a good verdict. But if the jury had convicted the two of separate and distinct offenses which they had severally committed, a different situation arises. 

Com. v. Wood, 12 Mass. 313

Two defendants indicted and tried jointly. The jury can agree on one and disagree on the other. If the crime cannot be separated, this cannot be done. 

Procedure in the return of verdict of jurors. See also under Jury. 

Com. v. Tobin, 125 Mass. 203

A verdict in this state must be properly rendered by word of mouth, and a sealed verdict handed by the foreman to the clerk of the Superior Court is not sufficient. 

Com. v. Walsh, 132 Mass. 8

It must appear in order to convict that the jury (which had agreed on a verdict and separated for the night) reported orally

CRIMINAL CASES 

527 the next morning the decision that they had come to the day before.

Some counts guilty, some counts not guilty. 

Com. v. Holmes, 137 Mass. 248

The complaint charged four counts of larceny. The judgment from the lower court charges that the defendant pleaded not guilty of the offense aforesaid. The plea, conviction, and sentence may be general and not upon each count. Even in the lower court, if the defendant was acquitted on three of the counts, he could be tried in the Superior Court on the other count. 

Consenting to a finding of guilty. 

Com. v. Ham, 150 Mass. 122

The defendant consented to a finding of guilty and in so doing waived all rights to exceptions. 

Two indictments, one verdict. 

Com. v. Hadad, 250 Mass. 391

One indictment for burning insured property with intent to defraud an insurance company. One indictment for maliciously burning a dwelling house. They were tried together. He was acquitted on the second indictment and found guilty on the first indictment. The offenses were not the same and the verdict was proper. Merger. 

Com. v. Andrews, 132 Mass. 263

It is no defense to an indictment that the evidence shows that the defendant committed a higher offense than the one charged in the indictment. 

Discharged on one count, convicted on the other. 

Com. v. Walsh, 255 Mass. 317

Two counts for perjury. Convicted on one count, acquitted on the other. Even if the evidence on the two counts was practically the same, the jury had the right to apply the evidence to one of the counts, and having exhausted its force by finding the defendant guilty on that count, were justified in returning the verdict of not guilty on the other.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Averment of adultery in a rape case. 

Com. v. Squires, 97 Mass. 59

The indictment charged a rape, but there was an allegation that the defendant was a married man. The jury rendered a verdict that the defendant had carnal knowledge but not against her will. The defendant was then rightly sentenced for adultery. 

Beyond a reasonable doubt. 

Com. v. Mason, 12 Allen, 185

Probability of guilt is not sufficient to authorize a conviction. 

The jury must be satisfied beyond a reasonable doubt. 

Where no value was alleged in larceny. 

Hope v. Com., 9 Met. 134

An indictment for larceny must state the value of the articles stolen, and if the complaint alleges stealing various articles but only states the collective value and the jury finds the defendant guilty of stealing only a part of them, no judgment can be legally rendered against him; if he was found guilty of the whole, perhaps different. 

Indicted for a felony, verdict of a misdemeanor. 

Com. v. Roby, 12 Pick. 496

Upon an indictment for a felony the defendant cannot be convicted of a misdemeanor. Overruling Com. v. Cooper, 15 Mass. 

187. But see Com. v. McCarthy, 165 Mass. 37 

Larceny from the person. 

Com. v. Lebowich, 235 Mass. 357

Larceny from the person was charged in two counts. A general verdict was good. 

Breaking and entering and larceny. 

Com. v. Clifford, 254 Mass. 390

The defendant was indicted and found guilty of breaking and entering in the night time and larceny therein. He can be sentenced for breaking and entering in the day time. 

Com. v. Lowery, 149 Mass. 67 

Breaking and entering and larceny. A general verdict may be rendered on larceny only.

CRIMINAL CASES 

529 

Com. v. Billings, 167 Mass. 283

Where a trial proceeds against one of two persons jointly indicted for breaking and entering and receiving stolen goods, he may be convicted of the whole or any part of the offense alleged. 

Jennings v. Com., 105 Mass. 586

Indictment for breaking and entering and larceny therein. 

Verdict was a general verdict of guilty "but not in the night time." The district attorney nol prossed so much of the indictment as charged breaking and entering and the defendant was sentenced for larceny. Libel. 

Com. v. Morgan, 107 Mass. 199

The jury found the defendant guilty of publishing, and not guilty of composing, writing, and printing. This is a general verdict of guilt. WAIVER 

Com. v. Maloney, 145 Mass. 205

The defendant can waive defects, but the consent of the defendant to appear does not give the court jurisdiction. 

Com. v. Rowe, 257 Mass. 172

A defendant may waive any matter of form or substance, excepting only what may relate to the jurisdiction of the court. 

WARRANT Graves' Case, 236 Mass. 493 

A warrant must be directed to an officer and not a class or department of officers, an organization, or an official or unofficial body.

WEAPONS, CARRYING GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 269, SEC. 10 AMENDED BY ACTS OF 1937, CHAP. 250 GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 140, SEC. 129A 

Com. v. Anderson, 245 Mass. 177

The defendant had the revolver in an automobile and he could rightly be held for possession.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Com. v. Mosciatiello, 257 Mass. 260

The owner of an automobile and the driver can be convicted of carrying a weapon which was under the back seat of the automobile. 

Five Cts. Sav. Bk. v. Searles, 237 Mass. 489

The police department has no right to retain a weapon of the owner who did not know that the defendant (in whose possession it was found) was carrying it. A suit in conversion may be maintained against the police officer. A bank messenger who was carrying the weapon was arrested for so doing. 

Com. v. O'Brien, 119 Mass. 342

On a trial for assault with a dangerous weapon there was evidence of an assault with a jackknife. The court ruled that a jackknife like the one exhibited to the jury was a dangerous weapon. 

Com. v. De Francesco, 248 Mass. 9

Form for alien carrying. That A, being then and there an unnaturalized foreign-born person, did have in his possession and under his control a certain fire arm, to wit, a revolver as defined in General Laws, Chapter 140, Section 121, without having a permit under Section 131 of said chapter. 

Com. v. O'Connor, 7 Allen, 583

A complaint for being armed when arrested by a police officer must show that the arrest was legal. 

Com. v. Drew, 4 Mass. 391

Whether a weapon is dangerous or not is a question for the jury. 

Com. v. Morgan, 280 Mass. 392 

A complaint charged an assault with a dangerous weapon does not fall within the provisions of Chapter 266, Section 15a, of the General Laws. That section charges an assault and battery by means of a dangerous weapon, which is a felony. The foregoing is a misdemeanor only.

WEIGHTS AND MEASURES GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 94, SEC. 177 

Com. v. Hunt, 216 Mass. 126

An offer of money to a deputy sealer of weights and measures is indictable, he being a municipal officer and having the authority to make complaints.

CRIMINAL CASES 

531 

Com. v. Sarks, 214 Mass. 72
