The law provides for the punishment for one who himself or by his servant or agent gives false weight. One can be convicted upon evidence of short weight in a sale made by a servant in the absence of the defendant. 

Com. v. Gussman, 215 Mass. 349 

The law providing that a barrel of potatoes (sweet) shall contain 150 pounds is constitutional and not against interstate commerce. 

Impeachment of a.

WITNESS 

GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 233 

Com. v. Murphy, 14 Mass. 387

Formerly a female who was a proved prostitute could be impeached, but this was overruled by Com. v. Churchill, 11 Met. 538. 

Com. v. Solomon, 234 Mass. 76

The introduction of a record to impeach the credibility of a witness by the introduction of a record under Chapter 233, Section 21, of the General Laws of a misdemeanor. Five years must elapse between the convictions and the time of testifying, or unless there has been a subsequent conviction of, the witness, within the period of five years prior to the time of testifying. 

Com. v. Vancenhecke, 248 Mass. 404

The fact that a female witness kept a house of ill fame is not admissible to impeach her. 

Ayers v. Ratchesky, 213 Mass. 589

The fact that the name of a person on the record is the same as the name of the person convicted does not make it admissible without corroboration of evidence of identity. 

Maloney v. Philpot, 219 Mass. 480

Offering a record to impeach the credibility of a witness on the rebuttal is too late, and then it is a matter of discretion of the court. 

Com. v. Walsh, 196 Mass. 369 

Com. v. Green, 17 Mass. 515 

Com. v. Sullivan, 161 Mass. 59

To affect the credibility of a witness, the record must be produced. 

See under Records for impeachment.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Confession of a witness. 

Com. v. Waite, 5 Mass. 261

As to his confession, the witness's incompetency to testify cannot be admitted to disqualify him. 

Insanity of witness. 

Com. v. Reagan, 175 Mass. 335

When a witness is objected to on account of insanity or youthfulness, it is the duty of the court to determine the question of incompetency and to reject unless it is satisfied that he is competent. 

Sullivan v. Justices of Court, 271 Mass. 435

Section 99 of Chapter 123 of the General Laws, which authorizes the justices to cause an investigation of the mental condition of any person coming before him, is applicable in civil as well as in criminal cases. 

Com. v. Zelenski, 287 Mass. 125

An insane person is not necessarily an incompetent witness. 

Age of witness. 

Com. v. Tatisos, 238 Mass. 322

The mere fact that a child is a little over six years of age does not prevent her from testifying; it is within the discretion of the court. 

Com. v. Marshall, 211 Mass. 86 It is within the discretion of the court to permit a child ten years old and unable to speak English to testify. 

Com. v. Hutchinson, 10 Mass. 225

A child under nine years of age was sworn as a witness. It is a question for the jury to observe her manner. 

Com. v. Ramage, 177 Mass. 349

A complaint for indecent assault on a child six years of age; within the discretion of the court as to her testifying. 

Leading questions. 

McLellan v. Fuller, 226 Mass. 374

Leading questions may be asked in direct examination; it is within the discretion of the court.

CRIMINAL CASES 

533 

Explaining record.

Lameroux v. N. Y. N. H. Ry., 169 Mass. 338 

A record of conviction put in evidence cannot be explained on re-examination. 

Refreshing recollection. 

Com. v. Ford, 130 Mass. 64 

Com. v. Thompson, 159 Mass. 56

A witness may be allowed, for the purpose of refreshing his recollection of what was said at a particular time, to look at a printed copy of his own written report of the proceedings at that time, although the absence of the written report is not accounted for. 

Com. v. Burton, 183 Mass. 461

It is not necessary for the writing to have been made by the witness himself, or that it should be the original writing, provided that after inspection, the witness can speak to the facts from his own recollection. 

Gurley v. St. Ry., 206 Mass. 534

A witness may refresh his recollection from records made by him, although the records are not in evidence. 

Com. v. Jeffs, 132 Mass. 5

A witness may be allowed to refresh his memory by looking at a written or printed paper, and if he thereby recalls a fact or circumstance he may testify to it. It is not the memorandum which is in evidence but the recollection of the witness. The paper cannot be put in evidence. 

Com. v. McDermott, 255 Mass. 575

A witness may use a paper to refresh his memory. It is immaterial that the paper is not contemporaneous with the events it records or that it is not made by the testifying witness. 

Com. v. Tucinski, 189 Mass. 257

One was on trial for manslaughter. Evidence of the general character of the deceased as a fighting man is admissible to show that the defendant had reason to fear bodily harm. 

Com. v. Lane, 151 Mass. 356

The complaint was for a nuisance, alleging from September 1 to September 9. The jury can refresh testimony from record sent on appeal from the lower court as to the date of the trial.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Absent witness. 

Com. v. Gallo, 275 Mass. 320

An absent witness's testimony at a previous trial can be introduced. Held to be competent. 

Deceased witness. 

Com. v. Glassman, 253 Mass. 65

Evidence of what a deceased witness testified to at a previous trial held to be competent. 

Contradiction of a witness. 

Com. v. Richmond, 207 Mass. 240

The commonwealth can contradict its own witness by other evidence. 

Com. v. Thyng, 134 Mass. 191

To contradict a witness and render the evidence admissible, the conditions of the statute must be complied with in every respect and questions cannot be asked generally, they must be specific. 

Com. v. Donahue, 133 Mass. 407

A party producing a witness may contradict his testimony in a matter material to the issue, by showing that he has made statements inconsistent with his present testimony, having just called the matter to his attention. 

Force v. Martin, 122 Mass. 5

A party cannot contradict his own witness by showing that he has at other times made statements inconsistent with his testimony, if his testimony is not material to the issue. 

Paid witness. 

Com. v. Graves, 97 Mass. 114

One who is paid to procure evidence and is paid a fee in liquor cases still is a competent witness. 

Incrimination of witness. 

Com. v. Pratt, 126 Mass. 462

A witness having voluntarily testified in court, testified in part on a matter tending to incriminate him. Cannot afterwards. decline to answer upon the same ground.

CRIMINAL CASES 

535 

Mayo v. Mayo, 119 Mass. 290

Where a witness is excused from answering questions on the grounds of incrimination and further questions asked showed that she did not understand, the court can call and instruct her and order that her evidence previously given be stricken out. 

Truth and veracity - reputation. 

Com. v. Perkins, 225 Mass. 80

A witness who has been subpoenaed to testify as to the reputation and character of another may be asked as to that person's truth and veracity but not as to his honesty and veracity. 

Subpoenaing a witness for the defense. 

Com. v. Williams, 13 Mass. 501

Except in capital cases the defendants in a criminal case must subpoena their own witnesses. 

Omission to caution witnesses. 

Com. v. Robinson, 165 Mass. 426

The omission to caution a witness when arrested that anything he might say could be used against him does not render his testimony incompetent. 

Furnishing against oneself. 

Emery's Case, 107 Mass. 172

The provisions of the Declaration of Rights that no subject shall be compelled to furnish or accuse himself as to evidence applies to an investigation by the legislature. 

Testifying not a bar to a subsequent prosecution. 

Com. v. Brown, 103 Mass. 422

The fact that a person confessed his guilt while testifying as a witness is not a bar to a prosecution growing out of the same case without express promise or protection by anyone.

Convicted in another state, record here admissible. 

Com. v. Knapp, 9 Pick. 496

The record of conviction in another state is admissible here to impeach.

CRIMINAL CASES 

Defendant waives privilege on taking stand. 

Com. v. Mullen, 97 Mass. 545 

Com. v. Smith, 163 Mass. 411

If the defendant takes the witness stand, he waives his constitutional rights and the privilege of refusing to furnish evidence against himself. 

Sick witness. Admission of testimony. 

Com. v. Donovan, 99 Mass. 425

On a motion for a continuance the defendant offered a certificate that a witness was ill and could not be present, but if present would testify as to certain things supported by an affidavit. The district attorney endorsed it and the court rightfully admitted it. 

Fees in a civil case. 

Atwood v. Scott, 99 Mass. 177

A witness in a civil case need not attend court after the time to which his fees have been paid or tendered to him. 

Religious belief. 

Thurston v. Whitney, 2 Cush. 104

One who does not believe in the existence of God is not a competent witness. This may be proved by competent witnesses. 

Discussion of the same by Wilde, J., on atheism. 

Com. v. Hills, 10 Cush. 530

It is a question whether a person is or is not an atheist; for the court to decide. 

Com. v. Burke, 16 Gray, 33

A witness cannot be examined as to his religious belief either upon the voir dire or cross-examination.

Malice of witness towards the defendant. 

Com. v. Putnam, 2 Allen, 301

Although it is proved that a witness in a criminal case is actuated by malice towards the defendant, the court should not instruct the jury to disregard his testimony altogether but leave it to the jury as to how much credit shall be given to it.

CRIMINAL CASES 

537 Confession of a witness before another tribunal. 

Com. v. King, 8 Gray, 501

The testimony of a witness before a fire inquest reduced to writing and signed by him is admissible against him on a trial for setting the fire. He must, however, be cautioned that he need not give testimony that might incriminate him. 

Objection to the competency, when. 

Shurtleff v. Willard, 19 Pick. 202

An objection to the competency of a witness should be made before he is examined in chief, but if it be not known until after such examination, it may be made at any stage of the trial when discovered.

One of two jointly tried may testify on a guilty plea. 

Com. v. Smith, 12 Met. 238 

Com. v. Fuller, 26 Mass. 329

Where two persons are jointly indicted and one of them pleads guilty, he is a competent witness before his sentence on the trial of the other.

One of two jointly indicted cannot testify, when. 

Com. v. Marsch, 10 Pick. 57

Where two are indicted jointly and the trial of one was postponed it was held that he could not be called as a witness for the other.

On a plea of guilty, witness may be asked. 

Dugura v. Phillips, 275 Mass. 283 

Com. v. Fortier, 258 Mass. 98

A witness may be asked as an admission of the fact charged whether he has pleaded guilty in a criminal matter. The question and answer are admissible if the admission is competent evidence. 

Cannot contradict on immaterial issues. 

Com. v. Buzzell, 16 Pick. 153

Evidence is not admissible to contradict a witness in regard to an immaterial fact, whether such fact comes out upon his examination or in cross-examination.

CRIMINAL CASES Exclusion of witness. 

Com. v. Crowley, 168 Mass. 121

The court has the right to exclude witnesses from the court during a trial. 

Com. v. Thompson, 159 Mass. 56

The court can refuse to separate witnesses until they have testified. 

Attendance of witness. 

Reid v. Wright, 181 Mass. 306

Where a witness is expected to testify and goes to an office in close proximity ready to go when called and is then allowed to go for the rest of the day, may fairly to be said to have attended as a witness for that day and a witness fee may be taxed. One need not attend all day to receive a fee. There is no such thing as a fraction of a day's attendance as a witness. 

Bar Association v. Scott, 209 Mass. 200 

There must be actual attendance at the court house or near it under circumstances which involve a real or appreciable interference with their everyday conduct. It is the attendance and not the willingness to attend. The mere fact that he holds himself in readiness supposing that he will be called is not an attendance. 

Definition of a witness. 

Horneman v. Brown, 286 Mass. at 70

The word "witness" naturally includes every person called to testify. It comprehends a party defendant in a criminal case. 

When a defendant can be called as a witness. 

Com. v. Fuller, 260 Mass. 329 The commonwealth cannot call the defendant to testify, but in a case where there are two defendants and one of them pleads guilty he then can be called and the court should charge that his plea cannot be taken into consideration as against the other defendant. 

Death of a medical examiner. 

Com. v. Caruse, 251 Mass. 362

The testimony of a medical examiner who dies before the trial in the Superior Court which was taken by two stenographers

CRIMINAL CASES 

539 and who was cross-examined by counsel for the defendant is properly admissible. 

Legislature can subpoena witnesses. 

Atty. Gen. v. Bressenden, 271 Mass. 172

The General Court has the right to make investigations in order to ascertain facts as a basis for legislation and for that purpose can subpoena witnesses and compel them to testify. Words.

CRIMINAL CASES WRIT OF CERTIORARI GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 249 Locke v. Lexington Selectmen, 122 Mass. 290 

A writ of certiorari lies only to correct the errors and restrain the excess of jurisdiction of inferior courts or officers acting judicially. 

Com. v. Sheldon, 3 Mass. 188

An order of notice issues and upon the return of the record the court will inspect it and if they find errors they will quash the proceedings. 

Lynch v. Crosby, 134 Mass. 313

A writ of certiorari does not lie to quasi proceedings of a lower court in the issuing of a warrant for the seizure of intoxicating liquors. It only lies to correct errors of inferior courts or officers acting judicially in proceedings not according to the course of the common law. 

Whitney v. Justices, 271 Mass. 448 

Prusik v. Board of Appeals, 262 Mass. 451

The function of a writ of certiorari is to enable a party without other remedy to bring the record of an inferior tribunal before the Supreme Court for examination as to mistakes of law apparent on the record.

WRIT OF ERROR GEN. LAWS, CHAP. 250 

Haynes v. Com., 107 Mass. 194

On a reversal of a sentence the defendant is entitled to costs. 

Shepard v. Com., 2 Met. 419 

Hurley v. Com., 188 Mass. 443

On reversing a sentence on a writ of error the Supreme Court cannot render a new verdict, or remit the case to the court below, but can only reverse the judgment and discharge the convict. Now changed Acts of 1851, Chapter 87. 

Cooke v. Com., 15 Pick. 234 

Britton v. Com., 1 Cush. 302

A writ of error lies to reverse the judgment of an inferior court against the defendant and not by a writ of certiorari.

CRIMINAL CASES 

543 

Thayer v. Com., 12 Met. 9

A writ of error lies to reverse the judgment of a justice of the peace in a criminal case, although judgment was open to an appeal. 

Harding v. Com., 283 Mass. 369

A judgment must be erroneous on the face of the record in order to be reversed on a writ of error. It is deemed to be good unless the error is shown. 

Sennott's Case, 146 Mass. 489 

Stalker v. Com., 167 Mass. 11

A writ of habeas corpus cannot perform the functions of a writ of error. A defendant was sentenced to be imprisoned and a clerical mistake was made in the mittimus. This will not avail the defendant nor declare the mittimus void. 

Com. v. Marsino, 252 Mass. 224

A petition of a writ of error must be dismissed if it was filed before judgment in the proceedings in which the error is alleged to have occurred. 

Com. v. Cummings, 3 Cush. 212

A writ of error does not lie in a criminal case in behalf of the commonwealth. 

Blankenburg v. Com., 260 Mass. 369

A matter of fact once tried and established by a tribunal having jurisdiction cannot be retried by a writ of error; the errors of fact in a judgment which may be reviewed upon a writ of error under Sections 3 and 9 of Chapter 250 relate to matters of fact not heard or decided at the trial under review. A writ of error will lie to correct errors in a proceeding and commitment for contempt of court. 

Com. v. Phelan, 271 Mass. 21

In this state the writ of error coram nobis (a writ of error on a judgment in the king's bench, a writ directed to the same court which tried the case) has become obsolete. 

WRIT TO ENJOIN 

Shuman v. Gilbert, 229 Mass. 225 

Harmon v. Police Com'r, 274 Mass. 56

Equity will not interfere with the prosecution of crimes unless such prosecutions are founded on unconstitutional or void

CRIMINAL CASES 

enactments and will result in immediate injury to property or property rights. This was an injunction to prevent the chief of police from arresting certain persons for being itinerant peddlers and the court refused to enjoin. The equity court will not prevent the issuance of search warrants every day for a certain place.

WRIT OF MANDAMUS 

Com. v. Sessions of Hampden, 2 Pick. at 418

A mandamus is a prerogative writ introduced to prevent disorder from a failure of justice and defect of the police; and therefore ought to be used on all occasions where the law has established no specific remedy and where in justice and good government there ought to be one. 

WRIT OF PROHIBITION 

Washburn v. Phillips, 2 Met. 296

Will issue only in cases where inferior courts have exceeded their authority. 

Cronin v. Justices of Court, 253 Mass. 182

The defendant was indicted, the jury impaneled, the case was taken from the jury, and a mistrial declared. Writ will not lie.

Goulis v. Justice of Court, 246 Mass. 1 

A writ will not lie for the correction of erroneous decisions of a court acting within its jurisdiction, but will issue only to restrain excess of jurisdiction. 

Donlon v. City Council, 238 Mass. 557

A writ will not issue to restrain a court or a tribunal which has jurisdiction over a matter or cause that it proposes to determine. It is granted to restrain the tribunal from wrongfully assuming jurisdiction, and it lies only for the purpose of preventing a clear excess of jurisdiction where there is no other adequate remedy. 

Hyde Park v. Wiggin, 157 Mass. 94

A writ will not issue to a court having jurisdiction merely because its proceedings are irregular.

CRIMINAL CASES 

545 

WRIT OF PROTECTION 

Com. v. McNeil, 3 Mass. 287

A writ is not necessary to protect one whose duty brings him to court as a witness, juror, or party, and will not protect one who is not lawfully entitled to it. 

MacNeill Pet'r, 6 Mass. 264

Where one attends court as a witness without being subpoenaed he is not privileged from arrest although he has a writ of protection. 

Com. v. Huggeford, 9 Pick. 257

One who holds a writ is immune from arrest civilly, for a reasonable period of time during the pendency in which he is subpoenaed. 

Chaffee v. Jones, 19 Pick. 260

Where a party entitled to freedom from arrest while returning from court went to a place out of the direct route for the purpose of attending the funeral of his son, he forfeited his privilege.

WRIT OF SCIRE FACIAS 

Com. v. Downey, 9 Mass. 520

A writ can only issue from a court in possession of the record upon which it issues. 

Gabis Pet'r, 240 Mass. 465

After sentence has been served, whether it was good or bad is moot; there is nothing on which the writ can operate.
